Figure 2
:
The torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is represented in the plane by a parallelogram with complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
and opposite sides pairwise identified and is shown with a choice of canonical homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
corresponding to the cycles
[0,1]
and
[0,\tau]
in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

2.5

Jacobi elliptic functions

For a given lattice
\Lambda
, the elliptic function
\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{i}
associated with a root
e_{i}
has a unique double pole at
z=0
and a unique double zero at
z=\omega_{i}/2
(mod
\Lambda
). The latter follows from the fact that its derivative
\wp^{\prime}(z|\Lambda)
vanishes at the half periods. Hence
\sqrt{\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{i}}
is a holomorphic function which is doubly periodic, up to sign factors, in
z
with periods
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
. The Jacobi elliptic functions
{\rm sn}(u|k),{\rm cn}(u|k)
, and
{\rm dn}(u|k)
may be defined in terms these square roots as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm sn}(u|k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{{e_{1}-e_{3}\over\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{3}}}\hskip 50.58878pt{\rm dn}(u|k)=\sqrt{{\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{2}\over\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{3}}}

\displaystyle{\rm cn}(u|k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{1}\over\wp(z|\Lambda)-e_{3}}}\hskip 50.58878ptk^{2}={e_{2}-e_{3}\over e_{1}-e_{3}}

(2.5.1)

The variables
u
and
z
, and the periods are related as follows,

\displaystyle u=\sqrt{e_{1}-e_{3}}\,z\hskip 72.26999ptK_{\alpha}=\omega_{\alpha}\,\sqrt{e_{1}-e_{3}}

(2.5.2)

When the value of
k
is clear from context, one uses the abbreviated notations
{\rm sn}(u)={\rm sn}(u|k)
,
{\rm cn}(u)={\rm cn}(u|k)
, and
{\rm dn}(u)={\rm dn}(u|k)
.
While the functions
{\rm sn}(u)^{2}
,
{\rm cn}(u)^{2}
,
{\rm dn}(u)^{2}
, and
{\rm sn}(u){\rm cn}(u){\rm dn}(u)
are doubly periodic under translations of
u
by the lattice
\Lambda\,\sqrt{e_{1}-e_{3}}
generated by the periods
K_{1},K_{2}
, the functions
{\rm sn}(u)
,
{\rm cn}(u)
, and
{\rm dn}(u)
are doubly periodic only up to signs in view of the square roots in their definition. Under
u\to-u
the function
{\rm sn}
is odd while
{\rm cn},{\rm dn}
are even. Their zeros and poles are simple and may be read off from the definition.
The modulus
k^{2}
plays a role for Jacobi elliptic functions analogous to the role played by the
j
-function for Weierstrass functions, and the two may be related to one another by expressing the roots
e_{\alpha}
in the definition of
k^{2}
in (
2.5
) using (
2.4.15
), giving,

\displaystyle j=256{(k^{4}-k^{2}+1)^{3}\over k^{4}(k^{2}-1)^{2}}

(2.5.3)

The following identities follow from the definitions of the Jacobi elliptic functions,

\displaystyle{\rm sn}(u)^{2}+{\rm cn}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle{\rm sn}^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-{\rm cn}(u)\,{\rm dn}(u)

\displaystyle k^{2}{\rm sn}(u)^{2}+{\rm dn}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle{\rm cn}^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-{\rm sn}(u)\,{\rm dn}(u)

\displaystyle{\rm dn}(u)^{2}-k^{2}{\rm cn}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=1-k^{2}

\displaystyle{\rm dn}^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-k^{2}{\rm sn}(u)\,{\rm cn}(u)

(2.5.4)

where the prime denotes the derivative with respect to
u
.
The differential relations were obtained with the help of the following relation,

\displaystyle\wp^{\prime}(z)=2(e_{1}-e_{3})^{{3\over 2}}{{\rm cn}(u)\,{\rm dn}(u)\over{\rm sn}(u)^{3}}

(2.5.5)

where the prime on the left side denotes the derivative with respect to
z
, and on the right side with respect to
u
. Upon taking the square of each differential relation and using the algebraic equations to express both sides in terms of the same function, we obtain the following differential equations,

\displaystyle{\rm sn}^{\prime}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-{\rm sn}(u)^{2}\right)\left(1-k^{2}{\rm sn}(u)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle{\rm cn}^{\prime}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-{\rm cn}(u)^{2}\right)\left(1-k^{2}+k^{2}{\rm cn}(u)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle{\rm dn}^{\prime}(u)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-{\rm dn}(u)^{2}\right)\left(k^{2}-1+{\rm dn}(u)^{2}\right)

(2.5.6)

An immediate corollary for Jacobi elliptic functions following from Theorem
2.4
is as follows.

Corollary 2.5

An arbitrary elliptic function
f(u)
with a lattice of periods
\Lambda\,\sqrt{e_{1}-e_{3}}
may be expressed as a rational function of either
{\rm sn}(u)^{2}
,
{\rm cn}(u)^{2}
, or
{\rm dn}(u)^{2}
which is linear in the product
{\rm sn}(u)\,{\rm cn}(u)\,{\rm dn}(u)
.

2.6

Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions

In the Weierstrass approach to elliptic functions, the basic building block is the meromorphic
\wp
-function and its derivative
\wp^{\prime}
, in terms of which every elliptic function is a rational function. Instead, the Jacobi
\vartheta
-function approach produces elliptic functions in terms of Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, which are holomorphic, at the cost of being multiple-valued on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. Scaling the lattice
\Lambda
so that
\omega_{1}=1
and
\omega_{2}=\tau
with
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
, the Jacobi
\vartheta
-function is defined by,

\displaystyle\vartheta(z|\tau)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{i\pi\tau n^{2}+2\pi inz}

(2.6.1)

and is often denoted simply by
\vartheta(z)
when the
\tau
-dependence is clear. The series is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
and defines a holomorphic function in
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The
\vartheta
-function satisfies a complexified version of the heat equation,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}\vartheta(z|\tau)=4\pi i\partial_{\tau}\vartheta(z|\tau)

(2.6.2)

The function
\vartheta(z|\tau)
is even in
z\to-z
and transforms under shifts in the lattice
\Lambda
by,

\displaystyle\vartheta(z+1|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta(z+\tau|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta(z|\tau)\,e^{-i\pi\tau-2\pi iz}

(2.6.3)

Thus,
\vartheta(z|\tau)
is not an elliptic function in
z
. Indeed, it could never be, as a holomorphic doubly periodic function must be constant. We shall later see how
\vartheta
can be naturally viewed as a holomorphic section of a line bundle on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, but for the time being we shall just work with it as a multiple-valued function.

2.6.1

The number of zeros of
\vartheta

To find the number of zeros of
\vartheta(z|\tau)
as a function of
z
, we integrate its logarithmic derivative along the closed boundary of the fundamental parallelogram
P_{0}
(with vertices
0,1,\tau,1+\tau
), and decompose the integration as a sum of the line integrals along the four edges,

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial P_{0}}dz\,\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta(z|\tau)=\left(\int_{0}^{1}+\int_{1}^{1+\tau}+\int_{1+\tau}^{\tau}+\int_{\tau}^{0}\right)dz\,\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta(z|\tau)

(2.6.4)

By periodicity of
\vartheta(z|\tau)
under
z\to z+1
, the contributions from the second and fourth integrals on the right side cancel
one another. The contribution of the third integral is just a translate and opposite of the first integral, so that we have,

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial P_{0}}dz\,\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta(z|\tau)=\int_{0}^{1}dz\Big(\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta(z|\tau)-\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta(z+\tau|\tau)\Big)

(2.6.5)

Using the second relation in (
2.6
), this integral is readily evaluated, and we find
2\pi i
which implies that
\vartheta(z|\tau)
has exactly one zero in
P_{0}
and, since it is holomorphic, no poles. To determine the position of this zero, it will be useful to introduce characteristicss.

2.6.2

\vartheta
-functions with characteristics

A convenient variant of the
\vartheta
-function introduced above is the
\vartheta
-function with characteristics
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}}
, defined by,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{i\pi\tau(n+\alpha)^{2}+2\pi i(n+\alpha)(z+\beta)}

(2.6.6)

It is related to the original
\vartheta
-function by translation of
z
and an exponential factor,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)=\vartheta(z+\alpha\tau+\beta|\tau)\,e^{i\pi\tau\alpha^{2}+2\pi i\alpha(z+\beta)}

(2.6.7)

The original
\vartheta(z|\tau)
-function corresponds to
\alpha=\beta=0
.
Under translations of
z
by the lattice
\Lambda
, and under a reflection
z\to-z
, we have,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z+1|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)\,e^{2\pi i\alpha}

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z+\tau|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)\,e^{-2\pi i\beta-i\pi\tau-2\pi iz}

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-\alpha\cr-\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](-z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

(2.6.8)

Under integer shift in the characteristics, the transformations are as follows,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha+1\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta+1\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)\,e^{2\pi i\alpha}

(2.6.9)

For a
\vartheta
-function with characteristics to have definite parity under
z\to-z
, we must have
-\alpha\equiv\alpha\,({\rm mod}\,1)
and
-\beta\equiv\beta\,({\rm mod}\,1)
, which requires
\alpha,\beta\in\{0,{1\over 2}\}
and corresponds to the four half-periods. It is traditional to give these four
\vartheta
-functions special names,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)=-\,\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}{1\over 2}\cr{1\over 2}\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}{1\over 2}\cr 0\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau)=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\cr 0\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)\hskip 18.06749pt

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\cr{1\over 2}\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right](z|\tau)

(2.6.10)

The minus sign for
\vartheta_{1}
is introduced for later convenience.
From the above discussion, it is manifest that
\vartheta_{1}
is odd under
z\to-z
while
\vartheta_{2},\vartheta_{3},\vartheta_{4}
are even. One designates the corresponding half-integer characteristics as odd and even characteristics respectively.

The parity of
\vartheta_{1}
means that we know the location of its zero, namely at
z=0
, and by periodicity we have zeros at every
z\in\Lambda
. Similarly, the zeros of
\vartheta_{2},\vartheta_{3}
, and
\vartheta_{4}
are respectively at
{1\over 2}+\Lambda
,
{1\over 2}+{\tau\over 2}+\Lambda
, and
{\tau\over 2}+\Lambda
. The function
\vartheta_{1}
is particularly useful, and we record here its monodromy properties in
z
for later use,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z+1|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z+\tau|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)\,e^{-i\pi\tau-2\pi iz}

(2.6.11)

2.6.3

The Riemann relations for Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions

There are four basic Riemann relations on Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, given by,

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa}\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle\,\prod_{i=1}^{4}\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{i})=2\,\prod_{i=1}^{4}\vartheta[\lambda](\zeta_{i}^{\prime})

(2.6.12)

where
\kappa
and
\lambda
are half-integer characteristics,

\displaystyle\kappa=\left[\begin{matrix}\kappa^{\prime}\cr\kappa^{\prime\prime}\end{matrix}\right]\hskip 72.26999pt\lambda=\left[\begin{matrix}\lambda^{\prime}\cr\lambda^{\prime\prime}\end{matrix}\right]

(2.6.13)

namely
\kappa^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\{0,{1\over 2}\}
. The sign factor
\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle
is given by,

\displaystyle\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle=\exp\{4\pi i(\kappa^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime\prime}-\kappa^{\prime\prime}\lambda^{\prime})\}

(2.6.14)

and the relation between
\zeta_{i}
and
\zeta_{i}^{\prime}
is given by,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta_{1}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{2}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{3}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{4}^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)=M\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta_{1}\cr\zeta_{2}\cr\zeta_{3}\cr\zeta_{4}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999ptM={1\over 2}\left(\begin{matrix}1&1&1&1\cr 1&1&-1&-1\cr 1&-1&1&-1\cr 1&-1&-1&1\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(2.6.15)

The proof of these Riemann relations is left as an exercise. An important special case is obtained by setting
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=z
and
\zeta_{3}=\zeta_{4}=w
so that
\zeta^{\prime}_{1}=z+w
,
\zeta_{2}^{\prime}=z-w
, and
\zeta^{\prime}_{3}=\zeta_{4}^{\prime}=0
for
i=2,3,4
. For this special case, the Riemann relation for
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}=0
becomes an addition formula,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\vartheta_{i}(z|\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{i}(w|\tau)^{2}=2\vartheta_{3}(z+w|\tau)\vartheta_{3}(z-w|\tau)\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)^{2}

(2.6.16)

and for
z=w
becomes a duplication formula,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)^{4}=2\vartheta_{3}(2z|\tau)\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)^{3}

(2.6.17)

Further setting
z=0
, we obtain the famous Jacobi formula,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)^{4}=0

(2.6.18)

whose validity guarantees the cancellation of the one-loop contribution to the cosmological constant in Type II and Heterotic string theories in flat Minkowski space-time.

2.6.4

Constructions of elliptic functions in terms of zeros and poles

There are various classic constructions of elliptic functions in terms of Weierstrass elliptic functions, Jacobi elliptic functions, and Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, in addition to the construction given in Theorem
2.4
. The most important ones of those will be reviewed here. The relations between these different constructions have direct physical applications, for example to Bose-Fermi equivalence in two-dimensional quantum field theory.

\bullet
The first construction is convenient when the zeros and poles of an elliptic function
f
are specified (subject to the conditions of Theorem
2.3
) and proceeds by taking ratios of products of
\vartheta_{1}
-functions. An elliptic function
f
must have the same number of zeros and poles in any fundamental parallelogram
P_{\mu}
by the second condition in Theorem
2.3
. We then have the following theorem.

Theorem 2.6

A meromorphic function
f(z|\tau)
with
N
poles
b_{i}
and
N
zeros
a_{i}
in
z
modulo
\Lambda
, with
i=1,\cdots,N
may be expressed in the following general form,

\displaystyle f(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}{\vartheta_{1}(z-a_{i}|\tau)\over\vartheta_{1}(z-b_{i}|\tau)}\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{i=1}^{N}(a_{i}-b_{i})\in\Lambda

(2.6.19)

for
a_{i},b_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, not necessarily mutually distinct.

To prove the theorem, we observe that the poles and zeros of both sides clearly match, the right side is manifestly periodic under
z\to z+1
, while periodicity in
z\to z+\tau
may be verified to hold using (
2.6.2
) if and only if the condition on
a_{i},b_{i}
of the theorem holds.

\bullet
The second construction is convenient when the poles of an elliptic function and their residues are given (subject to the conditions of Theorem
2.3
) and proceeds by using the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function and
\wp
-function, or equivalently by taking the logarithmic derivative of
\vartheta
-functions. The equivalence is made clear by the following relations, presented here for the rescaled lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
with periods
\omega_{1}=1,\omega_{2}=\tau
,

\displaystyle\wp(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta_{1}(\tau)-\partial_{z}^{2}\ln\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\zeta(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{1}(\tau)z+\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\ln\sigma(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)-\ln\vartheta_{1}(0|\tau)+\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{1}(\tau)z^{2}

(2.6.20)

The first relation follows from the fact that both sides have a double pole
1/z^{2}
and no other poles and are doubly periodic. The constant
\eta_{1}
may be determined in terms of
\vartheta
-functions by using the fact that
\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)
is odd in
z
and that
\wp(z|\tau)=z^{-2}+{\cal O}(z^{2})
, and we find,

\displaystyle\eta_{1}(\tau)=-{1\over 3}\,{\vartheta_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0|\tau)\over\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)}=2\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2}|\tau)

(2.6.21)

The second relation results from integrating the first and requiring
\zeta(z|\tau)
to be odd in
z
. Using (
2.4.3
), (
2.4.18
), and (
2.6
) one verifies that both sides have the same monodromy. The last equation follows from the second by integration and normalizing
\sigma(z)=z+{\cal O}(z^{3})
.

Theorem 2.7

An elliptic function
f(z|\tau)
with
N_{s}
poles
b_{i,s}
of order
s
and generalized residue
r_{i,s}
for
s=1,\cdots,S
and
i=1,\cdots N_{s}
may be represented as a sum of Weierstrass
\zeta
-functions and its derivatives, by identifying poles and generalized residues,

\displaystyle f(z|\tau)=r_{0}+\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}r_{i,s}\,\zeta^{(s-1)}(z-b_{i,s}|\tau)\hskip 57.81621pt\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}r_{i,1}=0

(2.6.22)

where
r_{0}
and
r_{i,s}
may depend on
\tau
but are independent of
z
, and
\zeta^{\prime}(z|\tau)=-\wp(z|\tau)
.

Finally, expressing
\wp(z|\tau)
as an infinite sum in (
2.4.1
), integrating this formula twice in
z
, and matching the integration constants, gives the product formula for the
\vartheta_{1}
-function and, by translations by half periods, for the remaining
\vartheta_{\alpha}
functions,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q^{1\over 8}\sin\pi z\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}e^{2\pi iz})(1-q^{n}e^{-2\pi iz})(1-q^{n})

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q^{1\over 8}\cos\pi z\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1+q^{n}e^{2\pi iz})(1+q^{n}e^{-2\pi iz})(1-q^{n})

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1+q^{n-{1\over 2}}e^{2\pi iz})(1+q^{n-{1\over 2}}e^{-2\pi iz})(1-q^{n})

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n-{1\over 2}}e^{2\pi iz})(1-q^{n-{1\over 2}}e^{-2\pi iz})(1-q^{n})

(2.6.23)

with
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
. We shall leave the derivation as an exercise.

2.7

Uniformization of cubics and quartics

Trigonometric functions may be viewed as parametrizing the points on the unit circle, specified by the quadratic equation
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
, by the functions
x=\sin t
and
y=\sin^{\prime}t=\cos t
. This process is referred to as uniformization.

Elliptic functions analogously parametrize all solutions to cubic and quartic equations.
The Weierstrass function
\wp(z|\Lambda)
uniformizes the following cubic equation,

\displaystyle y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}(\Lambda)x-g_{3}(\Lambda)=4(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})(x-e_{3})

(2.7.1)

by
x=\wp(z|\Lambda)
and
y=\wp^{\prime}(z|\Lambda)
in view of (
2.4.8
), where
g_{2}(\Lambda)
and
g_{3}(\Lambda)
are associated with the lattice
\Lambda
via (
2.4.9
) and (
2.4.5
). Analogously, the Jacobi elliptic function
{\rm sn}(u|k)
uniformizes the following quartic equation,

\displaystyle y^{2}=(1-x^{2})(1-k^{2}x^{2})

(2.7.2)

by
x={\rm sn}(u|k)
and
y={\rm sn}^{\prime}(u|k)
in view of the first equation in (
2.5
). The Jacobi elliptic functions
{\rm cn}
and
{\rm dn}
similarly uniformize different, but equivalent quartics. More generally, elliptic functions may be used to uniformize an arbitrary quartic of the form,

\displaystyle w^{2}=(v-v_{1})(v-v_{2})(v-v_{3})(v-v_{4})=v^{4}+c_{3}v^{3}+c_{2}v^{2}+c_{1}v+c_{0}

(2.7.3)

for arbitrary points
v_{1},\cdots,v_{4}\in{\mathbb{C}}
or, equivalently, for arbitrary coefficients
c_{0},\cdots,c_{3}\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
To show this, we make use of the fact that the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
acts conformally and bijectively on the compactified complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
by Möbius transformations. If the action of an arbitrary
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
transformation on the variables
v
and
v_{i}
is given by,

\displaystyle v={ax+b\over cx+d}\hskip 57.81621ptv_{i}={ax_{i}+b\over cx_{i}+d}\hskip 57.81621pt\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})

(2.7.4)

then
w
and the differences
v-v_{i}
obey simple transformation rules,

\displaystyle w={w_{0}\,y\over(cx+d)^{2}}\hskip 72.26999ptv-v_{i}={x-x_{i}\over(cx+d)(cx_{i}+d)}

(2.7.5)

which map the general quartic (
2.7.3
) in
(v,w)
into a quartic in
(x,y)
given by,

\displaystyle w_{1}^{2}\,y^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x-x_{1})(x-x_{2})(x-x_{3})(x-x_{4})

\displaystyle w_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w_{0}^{2}(cx_{1}+d)(cx_{2}+d)(cx_{3}+d)(cx_{4}+d)

(2.7.6)

Here
w_{0}
is an arbitrary constant in terms of which
w_{1}
is given by the second formula above.
The choice
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=(1,-1,k,-k)
with
w_{1}=1
reproduces the quartic (
2.7.2
). Taking the limit
x_{4}\to\infty
for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
and
4cw_{0}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}(ce_{i}+d)=-1
reproduces the cubic (
2.7.1
). This completes the proof that an arbitrary quartic may be uniformized either by the Weierstrass or the Jacobi elliptic functions.

2.8

Elliptic curves

Since an arbitrary lattice
\Lambda
is an Abelian subgroup of
{\mathbb{C}}
the lattice
\Lambda
is a normal subgroup of
{\mathbb{C}}
and the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is an Abelian group. Since
\Lambda
acts on
{\mathbb{C}}
without fixed points, the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is also a compact Riemann surface. Representing the torus in
{\mathbb{C}}
by a fundamental parallelogram
P_{\mu}
, the group law may be realized by addition in
{\mathbb{C}}
modulo the lattice
\Lambda
. The group structure requires an identity element which is just
0\in{\mathbb{C}}
modulo
\Lambda
, and an additive inverse of
z
which is just
-z
modulo
\Lambda
.

The procedure of uniformization, discussed in the preceding subsection, shows that elliptic functions naturally parametrize arbitrary cubics and quartics. In particular, the pair of coordinates
(x,y)=(\wp(z|\Lambda),\wp^{\prime}(z|\Lambda))
parametrizes the cubic curve
y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}(\Lambda)x-g_{3}(\Lambda)
in terms of the points in the torus
z\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. The group structure of
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
maps to an Abelian group structure on the corresponding elliptic curve, as we shall now explain in detail.

An elliptic curve
{\cal E}
for given
g_{2},g_{3}\in{\mathbb{C}}
is defined as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal E}=\Big\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{C}}\hbox{ such that }y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}\Big\}\cup\{P_{\infty}\}

(2.8.1)

The point at infinity
P_{\infty}=(\infty,\infty)
is added to obtain a compact space, just as the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is compact. While
y^{2}
is determined in terms of
x
by the equation, the sign of
y
is not, which is why the second entry
y
is required. For every generic value of
x
where
y\not=0,\infty
, there are two points in
{\cal E}
corresponding to
\pm y
. For the points
x=e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}=\infty
, however, we have
y=0,\infty
and there is only a single point in
{\cal E}
. These points correspond to branch points of the function
y=\sqrt{4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}}
where the two copies of
{\mathbb{C}}
intersect (see the left panel of Figure
3
). Alternatively, an elliptic curve may be defined in terms of a quartic,

\displaystyle{\cal E}=\Big\{(v,w)\in{\mathbb{C}}\hbox{ such that }w^{2}=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(v-v_{i})\Big\}\cup\{P_{+\infty},P_{-\infty}\}

(2.8.2)

The points
v=v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
correspond to the branch points of the function
w
where the two copies of
{\mathbb{C}}
intersect
(see the right panel of Figure
3
). In this representation two points at infinity
P_{\pm\infty}
are required, one on each sheet.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{4}
v_{3}
P_{+\infty}
P_{-\infty}
\mathfrak{A}
\mathfrak{B}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
e_{1}
e_{2}
P_{\infty}
e_{3}
\mathfrak{A}
\mathfrak{B}

Figure 3
:
The elliptic curve
{\cal E}
is represented in terms of a double cover of the Riemann sphere: with one branch point
P_{\infty}
at
\infty
in the left panel and with all four branch points finite in the right panel and two distinct points
P_{\pm\infty}
added at
\infty
to compactify the elliptic curve. A choice of canonical homology cycles
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
is indicated.

2.8.1

Embedding into
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}

Two complex-dimensional projective space
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
is a compact space defined by an equivalence relation
\approx
under rescaling coordinates in
({\mathbb{C}}^{3})^{*}={\mathbb{C}}^{3}\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
,

\displaystyle{\mathds{CP}}^{2}=\big\{(x,y,z)\in({\mathbb{C}}^{3})^{*}\hbox{ such that }(\lambda x,\lambda y,\lambda z)\approx(x,y,z)\hbox{ for }\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\big\}

(2.8.3)

Actually,
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
is a Kähler space isomorphic to
SU(3)/S(U(2)\times U(1))
. Every compact (orientable) Riemann surface may be embedded in
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
. In particular, the elliptic curve
{\cal E}
may be embedded in
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
by rendering its defining equation homogeneous in three variables, namely the original
x,y
as well as the additional variable
z
, to obtain,

\displaystyle{\cal E}=\Big\{(x,y,z)\in{\mathds{CP}}^{2}\hbox{ such that }y^{2}z=4x^{3}-g_{2}xz^{2}-g_{3}z^{3}\Big\}

(2.8.4)

Since
{\cal E}
is hereby defined as a closed subset of the compact space
{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
, it is compact without the need to add points at infinity. The original presentation of the elliptic curve
{\cal E}
is recovered in the coordinate patch
z=1
, while in the other two patches it is given by,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle y^{2}z

\displaystyle=4-g_{2}z^{2}-g_{3}z^{3}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=4x^{3}-g_{2}xz^{2}-g_{3}z^{3}

(2.8.5)

The last equation shows that the point
(0,1,0)\in{\mathds{CP}}^{2}
belongs to every elliptic curve
{\cal E}
. In fact the point
(0,1,0)
corresponds to the point
(x,y)=P_{\infty}
in (
2.8.1
). This may be seen by considering the large
x,y
behavior in the
z=1
patch and allowing for a suitable rescaling
(x,y,1)\approx(xy^{-1},1,y^{-1})\to(0,1,0)
as
x,y\to\infty
subject to
y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}
.

2.9

Addition formulas and group structure of elliptic curves

The additive group structure of the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
allows one to associate with two arbitrary points
P,Q\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
a third point
R\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
by requiring the sum of their complex coordinates
z_{P},z_{Q},z_{R}
to vanish
z_{P}+z_{Q}+z_{R}=0~({\rm mod~}\Lambda)
. The addition of the points
P
and
Q
gives
-R
. To obtain the corresponding operation on the elliptic curve in the Weierstrass presentation, we begin by obtaining the addition formula for the Weierstrass function, which generalizes the addition theorem of (
2.2.9
) for trigonometric functions,

\displaystyle\wp(z_{P}+z_{Q})+\wp(z_{P})+\wp(z_{Q})-{1\over 4}\left({\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})-\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})\over\wp(z_{P})-\wp(z_{Q})}\right)^{2}=0

(2.9.1)

To prove it, one begins by showing that the left side has no poles, so that it must be constant by Liouville’s theorem. One then shows that the constant vanishes by evaluating the expression at special points.
Specifically, for fixed generic
z_{Q}
, the function
\wp(z_{P}+z_{Q})
is an elliptic function in
z_{P}
with one double pole at
z_{P}=-z_{Q}\,({\rm mod}\,\Lambda)
. Thus, it is a rational function of
\wp(z_{P})
and
\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})
. Now
\wp(z_{P})-\wp(z_{Q})
has a simple zero at
z_{P}=-z_{Q}
, so it should occur to the power
-2
, but it also has a simple zero at
z_{P}=z_{Q}
, which is cancelled by multiplying by the square of
\wp^{\prime}(z_{P})-\wp^{\prime}(z_{Q})
. The combination has a double pole in
z_{P}
at
z_{P}=0
which is cancelled by the addition of
\wp(z_{P})
and, by symmetry under the interchange of
z_{P}
and
z_{Q}
, also
\wp(z_{Q})
. Thus the left side has no poles and is therefore constant. To evaluate the constant, we set
z_{P}=\omega_{1}/2
and
z_{Q}=\omega_{2}/2
, use the fact that
\wp^{\prime}
vanishes at the non-zero half-periods, and then use the first formula in (
2.4.12
).

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x
y
P
Q
R

Figure 4
:
The real section of the elliptic curve
y^{2}=4x^{3}-4x+4
in black, the straight line
9y=10x+14.72
in red, and their three intersection points
P,Q,R
.

On the elliptic curve
{\cal E}
we represent the points
P,Q,R
by the coordinates
(x_{P},y_{P})
,
(x_{Q},y_{Q})
and
(x_{R},y_{R})
respectively and, for simplicity, we assume that the points
P
and
Q
are distinct. A straight line
y=\alpha x+\beta
through the two distinct points
P,Q
on
{\cal E}
intersects the cubic
{\cal E}
at exactly one further point
R
. Therefore, its coordinates
(x_{R},y_{R})
satisfy,

\displaystyle y_{R}={y_{P}-y_{Q}\over x_{P}-x_{Q}}\,x_{R}+{x_{P}y_{Q}-x_{Q}y_{P}\over x_{P}-x_{Q}}\hskip 72.26999pty_{R}^{2}=4x_{R}^{3}-g_{2}x_{R}-g_{3}

(2.9.2)

This system of equations may be solved for
x_{R}
as follows,

\displaystyle x_{R}+x_{P}+x_{Q}-{1\over 4}\left({y_{P}-y_{Q}\over x_{P}-x_{Q}}\right)^{2}=0

(2.9.3)

with
y_{R}
given by the second equation of (
2.9.2
). Parametrizing the coordinates by
x=\wp(z)
and
y=\wp^{\prime}(z)
evaluated at
z=z_{P},z_{Q},z_{R}
, we recover the addition law (
2.9.1
) for the Weierstrass function. The point
P_{\infty}
plays the role of the unit element of the additive group
{\cal E}
. This may be seen by letting
x_{Q},y_{Q}\to\infty
which implies
(x_{R},y_{R})=(x_{P},y_{P})
.

2.10

Meromorphic functions and Abelian differentials

Much of the work on elliptic functions in the 19-th century was motivated by the study of elliptic integrals, namely definite or indefinite integrals involving the square root of a cubic or quartic polynomial. In this subsection we shall consider meromorphic functions and Abelian differentials before addressing their integrals in the subsequent subsection. For a discussion of differentials and their properties on arbitrary Riemann surfaces, see appendix
B
.

The space of meromorphic functions on the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
is the field
{\mathbb{C}}(x)
of rational functions of a holomorphic coordinate
x\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Meromorphic functions on an elliptic curve
{\cal E}
correspond to elliptic functions on the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. Since elliptic functions on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
are rational functions of
\wp(z)=\wp(z|\Lambda)
and
\wp^{\prime}(z)=\wp^{\prime}(z|\Lambda)
, the space of meromorphic functions on an elliptic curve
{\cal E}
is the field
{\mathbb{C}}(x,y)
of rational functions of
x
and
y
. The field
{\mathbb{C}}(x,y)
may be viewed as a quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{C}}(x)
by
y
, since
y^{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}(x)
on an elliptic curve
{\cal E}
. The Galois group of this extension is
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, the group of involutions of
{\cal E}
that maps
(x,y)\to(x,-y)
and swaps the two sheets in the elliptic representation of Figure
3
. On the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
involution simply corresponds to
z\to-z
(mod
\Lambda
).

2.10.1

Perspective of the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda

To investigate meromorphic differentials we first consider the perspective of the torus. On a torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with local complex coordinate
z
, there exists a translation invariant
(1,0)
-form
dz
which is unique up to a multiplicative constant. The differential
dz
is holomorphic and nowhere vanishing. As a result, the vector spaces of meromorphic
(n,0)
-forms for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are all isomorphic to the space of meromorphic
(0,0)
-forms, i.e. elliptic functions. Thus, the most general meromorphic differential
(n,0)
-form on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is given by
f(z)(dz)^{n}
where
f(z)
is an elliptic function on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. Using the general construction of elliptic functions in terms of the Weierstrass functions, Theorems
2.4
and
2.7
allow us to express an arbitrary meromorphic
(n,0)
-form
\varpi=f(z)(dz)^{n}
as a rational function in
\wp
and
\wp^{\prime}
which may be chosen to be linear in
\wp^{\prime}
or, equivalently, as a sum of derivatives of the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function,

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A\big(\wp(z)\big)(dz)^{n}+B\big(\wp(z)\big)\,\wp^{\prime}(z)(dz)^{n}

(2.10.1)

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{0}\,dz+\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}r_{i,s}\,\zeta^{(s-1)}(z-b_{i,s})\,dz\hskip 57.81621pt\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}r_{i,1}=0

(2.10.2)

where
A
and
B
are rational functions of their argument, while the poles
b_{i,s}
and the coefficients
r_{0},r_{i,s}
are arbitrary aside from the vanishing of the sum of
r_{i,1}
. We shall denote the vector space of meromorphic
(n,0)
-forms
\Omega_{n}=\Omega_{n}({\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda)
.

The space
d\Omega_{0}
of
(1,0)
-forms obtained by taking the differential of elliptic functions is a subspace
d\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega_{1}
. This may be seen in the representation (
2.10.1
) by taking the differential of a general elliptic function
f\in\Omega_{0}
and then using the differential equation (
2.4.8
) and its derivative to recast the result again in the form (
2.10.1
). In the representation of (
2.10.2
) the differential kills the first term and simply increases the order of the derivatives in the terms under the double sum, thereby producing a
(1,0)
-form of the type (
2.10.2
).

However, not every meromorphic
(1,0)
-form is the differential of an elliptic function. Constructing the space
\Omega_{1}/d\Omega_{0}
of meromorphic
(1,0)
modulo differentials of elliptic functions is a cohomology problem whose solution is fundamental in the theory of Riemann surfaces and in string theory. To obtain the generators of
\Omega_{1}/d\Omega_{0}
we express an arbitrary
f\in\Omega_{0}
and
\varpi\in\Omega_{1}
in terms of the representation (
2.10.2
), and then take the differential of
f
,

\displaystyle df

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{N}_{s}}\tilde{r}_{i,s}\,\zeta^{(s)}(z-\tilde{b}_{i,s})\,dz

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{N}_{1}}\tilde{r}_{i,1}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle=r_{0}\,dz+\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{s}}r_{i,s}\,\zeta^{(s-1)}(z-b_{i,s})\,dz

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}r_{i,1}

\displaystyle=0

(2.10.3)

All terms in
\varpi
involving
\zeta^{(s-1)}(z-b_{i,s})
for
s\geq 3
may be matched by terms in
df
provided we choose the poles
\tilde{b}_{i,s-1}=b_{i,s}
and coefficients
\tilde{r}_{i,s-1}=r_{i,s}
. For
s=2
, this would also be the case if it were not for the constraint on the sum of the
\tilde{r}_{i,1}
, which means that a single
\zeta^{(1)}(z-b|\tau)
is, manifestly, not the differential of an elliptic function. The term
r_{0}dz
and the terms proportional to
\zeta(z-b_{i,1})
are not differentials of elliptic functions but, in view of the vanishing sum of residues, always occur as differences
\zeta(z-b_{i,1})-\zeta(z-b_{j,1})
. In summary,
\Omega_{1}/d\Omega_{0}
is generated by three type of
(1,0)
-forms, referred to as Abelian differentials.

1.

Abelian differential of the first kind: holomorphic and given by a constant multiple of the coordinate differential
dz
;

2.

Abelian differentials of the second kind: have one double pole at an arbitrary point
z_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
and no other poles and are given by a constant multiple of

\displaystyle\wp(z-z_{i})dz=-\zeta^{(1)}(z-z_{i})dz

(2.10.4)

3.

Abelian differentials of the third kind: have two simple poles with residues
\pm 1
at arbitrary points
z_{i}\not=z_{j}\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
and no other poles and are given in terms of the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function by,

\displaystyle\big(\zeta(z-z_{i})-\zeta(z-z_{j})+\zeta(z_{i}-z_{j})\big)dz

(2.10.5)

We note that the third term inside the parentheses is included to make the differential invariant under
\Lambda
not only in
z
but also in the points
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
.

The characterization of Abelian differentials of the second and third kind in terms of their poles does not specify them uniquely as one may add an arbitrary multiple of
dz
to either.

2.10.2

Perspective of the elliptic curve
{\cal E}

For an elliptic curve
{\cal E}
with local coordinates
(x,y)
given in terms of
g_{2},g_{3}\in{\mathbb{C}}
by,

\displaystyle y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}

(2.10.6)

the
(1,0)
form
dz
on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
may be expressed in terms of
(x,y)=(\wp(z|\Lambda),\wp^{\prime}(z|\Lambda))
by using the relation (
2.4.8
) and we find,

\displaystyle dz={dx\over y}={dx\over\sqrt{4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}}}

(2.10.7)

The differential
dx/y
is single-valued and holomorphic on
{\cal E}
since the square root at one of the branch points
e_{i}
is well-defined in a suitable local coordinate
\xi
with
\xi^{2}=x-e_{i}
, in which the differential is given by

dx/y=c_{i}^{-1}d\xi+{\cal O}(\xi)
where
c_{i}^{2}=(e_{i}-e_{j})(e_{i}-e_{k})
for
j,k\not=i
. The coordinate differential
dx
vanishes at the branch points
e_{i}
and has a triple pole at
\infty
.

The differential
dz
may also be obtained for an arbitrary quartic of the form (
2.7.3
),

\displaystyle{dv\over w}\hskip 72.26999ptw^{2}=(v-v_{1})(v-v_{2})(v-v_{3})(v-v_{4})

(2.10.8)

The differential
dx/y
is mapped to
dv/w
by the
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
Möbius transformation of (
2.7.4
) which maps
(x,y)
to
(v,w)
,
x_{i}
to
v_{i}
, and the differential
dx
to
dv=dx/(cx+d)^{2}
.

An arbitrary meromorphic differential
\omega
on the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, expressed in terms of
\wp
and
\wp^{\prime}
in (
2.10.1
), translates to the following expression on the corresponding elliptic curve
{\cal E}
,

\displaystyle\omega=A(x){dx\over y}+B(x)dx

(2.10.9)

where
A
and
B
are rational functions of
x
. Under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
involution of
{\cal E}
which maps
(x,y)\to(x,-y)
, the first term in
\omega
is odd while the second term is even. We leave it as an exercise to show that the Abelian differentials map to the elliptic curve
{\cal E}
as follows,

1.

Abelian differential of the first kind: a constant multiple of
dx/y
;

2.

Abelian differentials of the second kind: have one double pole at an arbitrary point
(x_{i},y_{i})\in{\cal E}
and no other poles and, using the addition formula of (
2.9.1
), are given by,

\displaystyle\left(-x-x_{i}+{1\over 4}{(y+y_{i})^{2}\over(x-x_{i})^{2}}\right){dx\over y}

(2.10.10)

3.

Abelian differentials of the third kind: have two simple poles with opposite residues
\pm 1
at arbitrary points
(x_{i},y_{i})\not=(x_{j},y_{j})
and no other poles and are given by,

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\left({y+y_{i}\over x-x_{i}}+{y_{j}+y\over x_{j}-x}+{y_{i}+y_{j}\over x_{i}-x_{j}}\right){dx\over y}

(2.10.11)

The last term inside the parentheses arises from the
\zeta(z_{i}-z_{j})dz
contribution in the torus representation, and is proportional to the Abelian differential of the first kind.

2.11

Abelian and elliptic integrals

The decomposition of the space of meromorphic
(1,0)
-forms
\Omega_{1}
into a sum of the space of exact differentials of elliptic functions
d\Omega_{0}
plus the three kinds of basic Abelian differentials greatly simplifies the problem of integration of
(1,0)
-forms. Integration of exact differentials
\varpi\in d\Omega_{0}
is straightforward, and we shall now concentrate on the integration of the three kinds of Abelian differentials. A canonical basis of first homology
generators of the elliptic curve
{\cal E}
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
and was shown already in Figures
2
and
3
. Parametrizing the lattice
\Lambda=\omega_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\omega_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
by the periods
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
satisfying
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
with
\tau\not\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the homology basis may be represented on the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
by the following simple cycles,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}

\displaystyle:\hskip 14.45377pt

\displaystyle z\to z+\omega_{1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle:\hskip 14.45377pt

\displaystyle z\to z+\omega_{2}

(2.11.1)

We shall study integrals of Abelian differentials along open and closed paths in
{\cal E}
and
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. We consider open paths between arbitrary points
z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
corresponding to the points
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in{\cal E}
, related by the Weierstrass function
x_{i}=\wp(z_{i}),y_{i}=\wp^{\prime}(z_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

2.11.1

Abelian integrals of the first kind

The integral of the Abelian differential
dz=dx/y
of the first kind is given as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{(x_{1},y_{1})}^{(x_{2},y_{2})}{dx\over\sqrt{4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}}}=\int_{(x_{1},y_{1})}^{(x_{2},y_{2})}{dx\over y}=\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}dz=z_{2}-z_{1}

(2.11.2)

Viewed as taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}
, the integral depends on the path of integration. Indeed, while holomorphicity of
dz=dx/y
guarantees independence under small deformations of the path of integration, the integral will depend on how many times the path circles the cycles
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
. The integrals of
dz=dx/y
around
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
give the periods of the lattice
\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}{dx\over y}=\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz=\omega_{1}\hskip 72.26999pt\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}{dx\over y}=\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}dz=\omega_{2}

(2.11.3)

Thus, while the value of the integral of (
2.11.2
) depends on the path of integration when its range is
{\mathbb{C}}
, the integral becomes single-valued as a map to
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
.

2.11.2

Abelian integrals of the second kind

The integral of the Abelian differential of the second kind,

\displaystyle\varpi=\wp(z-z_{i})dz=\left(-x-x_{i}+{1\over 4}{(y+y_{i})^{2}\over(x-x_{i})^{2}}\right){dx\over y}

(2.11.4)

is, by definition, given by the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function in view of
\zeta^{\prime}(z)=-\wp(z)
,

\displaystyle\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\varpi=\zeta(z_{1}-z_{i})-\zeta(z_{2}-z_{i})

(2.11.5)

The integral depends on the path of integration and the number of times the path winds around the cycles
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, which may be evaluated using (
2.4.3
),

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}\varpi=-\eta_{1}\hskip 57.81621pt\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}\varpi=-\eta_{2}

(2.11.6)

These periods do not belong to the lattice
\Lambda
, as is clear from the relation (
2.4.18
). In fact, it is not possible to form a linear combination of
\varpi
and the Abelian differential of the first kind
dz
to obtain a single-valued integral taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. But it is possible to form combinations with either vanishing
\mathfrak{A}
period or vanishing
\mathfrak{B}
period. For example
\varpi+\eta_{1}dz/\omega_{1}
has vanishing
\mathfrak{A}
period while its
\mathfrak{B}
period is
2\pi/\omega_{1}
.

2.11.3

Abelian integrals of the third kind

The integral of the Abelian differential of the third kind,

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\zeta(z-z_{i})+\zeta(z_{i}-z_{j})+\zeta(z_{j}-z)\Big)dz

(2.11.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\left({y+y_{i}\over x-x_{i}}+{y_{i}+y_{j}\over x_{i}-x_{j}}+{y_{j}+y\over x_{j}-x}\right){dx\over y}

is given by the logarithm of the Weierstrass
\sigma
-function in view of
\sigma(z)^{\prime}/\sigma(z)=\zeta(z)
,

\displaystyle\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\varpi=\ln{\sigma(z_{1}-z_{i})\sigma(z_{2}-z_{j})\over\sigma(z_{2}-z_{i})\sigma(z_{1}-z_{j})}

(2.11.8)

Its period integrals are given by,

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}\varpi=\omega_{1}\eta_{1}(z_{i}-z_{j})\hskip 72.26999pt\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}\varpi=\omega_{2}\eta_{1}(z_{i}-z_{j})

(2.11.9)

For the canonical periods
\omega_{1}=1
and
\omega_{2}=\tau
, we may convert this expression in terms of Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, using (
2.6
), and we obtain,

\displaystyle\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\varpi=\ln{\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-z_{i})\vartheta_{1}(z_{2}-z_{j})\over\vartheta_{1}(z_{2}-z_{i})\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-z_{j})}+\eta_{1}(z_{2}-z_{1})(z_{i}-z_{j})

(2.11.10)

We conclude that all elliptic integrals can be evaluated in terms of Weierstrass and Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The early history of the development of elliptic functions and modular forms, with emphasis on the contributions of Eisenstein and Kronecker, is discussed in the delightful book by Weil
[
2
]
. Classic treatises on Weierstrass elliptic functions, Jacobi elliptic functions, and Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions may be found in Whittaker and Watson
[
3
]
as well as in the Bateman manuscript
[
1
]
, where many practical definitions and useful formulas are collected. Mumford
[
4
]
emphasizes Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, while Lang
[
5
]
deals with elliptic functions as well as modular forms. An account of applications of elliptic functions to physics and technology may be found in a classic book by Oberhettinger and Magnus
[
6
]
.

3

Modular forms for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

In the preceding section, we studied the dependence of elliptic functions on the points in the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. In this section, we shall investigate the dependence on the lattice
\Lambda
. We begin by discussing the equivalence relations which lead to the transformation laws of elliptic functions, Eisenstein series, and
\vartheta
-functions under the modular group. We shall also discuss the Poincaré upper half-plane and the fundamental domain of the modular group.

3.1

Automorphisms of lattices and the modular group

A lattice
\Lambda
in the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
may be generated by a pair of periods
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
which are linearly independent over
{\mathbb{R}}
, namely such that
\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}\not\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We shall be interested in orientable surfaces
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, and choose a definite orientation so that the pair of periods
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is ordered by choosing
{\rm Im\,}(\omega_{2}/\omega_{1})>0
.

The lattice
\Lambda
, and thus the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, may be generated equivalently by other choices of periods, such as for example by
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}+n\omega_{1}
for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. More generally,
\Lambda
is equivalently generated by any pair of periods
\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}
given by a linear combination of
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
with integer coefficients,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\omega_{2}^{\prime}\cr\omega_{1}^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)=\gamma\left(\begin{matrix}\omega_{2}\cr\omega_{1}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 43.36243pta,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(3.1.1)

provided
\gamma
has an inverse with integer entries. This condition is equivalent to
{\rm det\,}\gamma=\pm 1
, or more formally
\gamma\in GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. On the ratio
\tau
of periods, which is referred to as the modulus of the lattice
\Lambda
or the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, a transformation
\gamma\in GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
acts as follows,

\displaystyle\tau={\omega_{2}\over\omega_{1}}\hskip 57.81621pt\tau^{\prime}={\omega_{2}^{\prime}\over\omega_{1}^{\prime}}\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma\tau=\tau^{\prime}={a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}

(3.1.2)

A transformation
\gamma\in GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
will preserve the orientation
{\rm Im\,}(\omega_{2}/\omega_{1})={\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
of the lattice provided the new periods enjoy the same orientation
{\rm Im\,}(\omega_{2}^{\prime}/\omega_{1}^{\prime})={\rm Im\,}(\tau^{\prime})>0
.
Using the above transformation rule, the imaginary parts are found to transform as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm Im\,}(\tau^{\prime})={{\rm det\,}\gamma\over|c\tau+d|^{2}}\,{\rm Im\,}(\tau)

(3.1.3)

The orientation of the lattice
\Lambda
will be preserved provided
\gamma
is restricted to the
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
subgroup of
GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
for which
{\rm det\,}\gamma=1
, promoting
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
to the group of orientation-preserving automorphisms of
\Lambda
. The transformation
-I\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
maps the periods into their opposites, but leaves their ratio
\tau
invariant. Thus, the faithful action on the lattice
\Lambda
is by
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but on
\tau
it is by the group
PSL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
where
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=\{\pm I\}
.

3.1.1

Structure of the modular group

The group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is an infinite discrete non-Abelian group, referred to as the modular group. It is generated by two of its elements,

\displaystyle S=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\cr 1&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999ptT=\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\cr 0&1\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(3.1.4)

These generators satisfy three fundamental relations,

\displaystyle S^{2}=(ST)^{3}=(TS)^{3}=-I

(3.1.5)

Any
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
can be written as a finite word in the letters
S,T
. By using the first of the above relations, words with two consecutive
S
letters may be simplified and omitted when listing independent elements. Thus, any
\gamma
has the following product decomposition,

\displaystyle\gamma=\pm T^{\alpha_{1}}ST^{\alpha_{2}}S\,\cdots\,ST^{\alpha_{n}}

(3.1.6)

for
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and some positive integer
n
. Furthermore, any word with three consecutive combinations of
ST
or
TS
may also be omitted by the last two relations.

To prove the validity of this decomposition, we consider an arbitrary
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pta,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\,\,~ad-bc=1

(3.1.7)

If
c=0
, then
ad=1
, and
\gamma=aT^{ab}
. If
c<0
we multiply
\gamma
by
-I
to reduce this case to the case
c>0
. For
c\geq 1
we proceed by induction on
c
. If
c=1
, then
ad-b=1
and we have
\gamma=T^{a}ST^{d}
. Thus, the proposition holds true for
c=0,1
. If
c\geq 2
we shall assume that the proposition holds true for all matrices whose lower left entry is positive and less than
c
, and the proposition remains to be proven for all matrices whose left lower entry equals
c
. Since
c\geq 2
,
c
cannot divide
a
, leaving the following two possibilities:

•

If
|a|>c
, then there exists a non-zero integer
n
such that
a=nc+r
with
0<r<c
, and the matrix
\gamma^{\prime}=ST^{-n}\gamma
has lower left entry equal to
r<c
.

•

If
|a|<c
, then the matrix
\gamma^{\prime}={\rm sign}(a)S\gamma
has lower left entry
|a|<c
.

In either case, by applying a product of
S
and
T
generators we have constructed from
\gamma
a new matrix with strictly smaller value of the lower left entry. The validity of the proposition follows by induction on the value of
c
.

3.2

The fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

In this subsection, we begin with a brief review of the geometry of the Poincaré upper half-plane, and then discuss the construction of the fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
as well as its fixed points and cusps.

3.2.1

The Poincaré upper half-plane

We denote the Poincaré upper half-plane by,

\displaystyle{\cal H}=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{C}},~{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0\}

(3.2.1)

Under the action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
by Möbius transformations on
\tau
, the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
is mapped to itself and its boundary
\partial{\cal H}={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
is mapped to itself. The isotropy group of an arbitrary point in
{\cal H}
is isomorphic to
SO(2)
and hence
{\cal H}
is the symmetric space,

\displaystyle{\cal H}=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2)

(3.2.2)

For example, the elements which leave
\tau=i
invariant obey
i=(ai+b)/(ci+d)
and
ad-bc=1
with
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}}
and may be parametrized by
b=-c,d=a
, and
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
to form a group isomorphic to
SO(2)
. The Poincaré metric on
{\cal H}
, defined by the line element,

\displaystyle ds^{2}={|d\tau|^{2}\over({\rm Im\,}\tau)^{2}}

(3.2.3)

is an
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant Riemannian metric of constant negative curvature. The Poincaré upper half-plane equipped with the Poincaré metric is a model of non-Euclidean geometry. Its geodesics (or straight lines) are the half-circles of arbitrary radius centered on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Given a geodesic
a
and a point
p
not on this geodesic, there are an infinite number of geodesics through
p
that do not intersect
a
and are thus parallel to
a
, as illustrated in Figure
5
.

{\cal H}
{\mathbb{R}}
\bullet
p
a
b
c
D
\beta
\alpha
{\cal H}
{\mathbb{R}}

Figure 5
:
In the left figure the geodesics of the Poincaré upper half-plane
{\cal H}
are shown as the semi-circles centered on
{\mathbb{R}}
with arbitrary radius; the geometry is non-Euclidean as several geodesics through a given point
p
, such as
b
and
c
, are parallel to the geodesic
a
. In the right figure, the area enclosed by the semi-infinite triangle
D
is
{\rm area}(D)=\pi-\alpha-\beta
.

The area of a polygon whose sides are geodesics may be obtained by decomposing the area into sums and differences of semi-infinite triangles with one vertex at the cusp and two corners with opening angles
\alpha
and
\beta
, as shown in Figure
5
,

\displaystyle{\rm area}(D)=\int_{D}{d^{2}\tau\over({\rm Im\,}\tau)^{2}}=\pi-\alpha-\beta\hskip 72.26999ptd^{2}\tau={i\over 2}d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}

(3.2.4)

The area of a finite triangle with opening angles
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
is then
\pi-\alpha-\beta-\gamma
, and is clearly always less than
\pi
, which is a well-known characteristic of hyperbolic geometry.

3.2.2

The fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

Since the modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is a subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, it also maps
{\cal H}
to
{\cal H}
and
\partial{\cal H}
to
\partial{\cal H}
. Every oriented lattice
\Lambda
, or equivalently oriented torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, may be specified by a point
\tau\in{\cal H}
up to overall rescaling of the lattice. Since the lattices corresponding to two points
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are equivalent to one another if
\tau^{\prime}=\gamma\tau
with
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, the space of inequivalent lattices or inequivalent tori is given by the quotient,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2)

(3.2.5)

Just as we represented the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
by a fundamental parallelogram in
{\mathbb{C}}
, we can represent the coset
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}
by a fundamental domain
F
in
{\cal H}
, which is a connected subset of
{\cal H}
. The fundamental domain is not unique since, for example, if
F
is a fundamental domain then so if
\gamma F
. The standard choice is given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1

The following
F
is a fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle F=\left\{\tau\in{\cal H},~|\tau|\geq 1,~|{\rm Re\,}(\tau)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\right\}

(3.2.6)

where it is understood that the boundary components at
{\rm Re\,}(\tau)=\pm{1\over 2}
are identified under
\tau\to\tau+1
while the boundary components at
|\tau|=1
for
{\rm Re\,}(\tau)
positive and negative are identified with one another under
\tau\to-1/\tau
(see Figure
6
). The area of
F
in the Poincaré metric is given by
{\rm area}(F)={\pi\over 3}
in view of (
3.2.4
).

To prove the theorem we show that for an arbitrary point
\tau\in{\cal H}
there exists a transformation
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
\gamma\tau\in F
. To do so, we fix
\tau
and make use of the relation,

\displaystyle{\rm Im\,}(\gamma\tau)={{\rm Im\,}(\tau)\over|c\tau+d|^{2}}

(3.2.7)

to construct a
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
with the largest possible value of
{\rm Im\,}(\gamma\tau)
. This is done by minimizing
|c\tau+d|
for fixed
\tau
as
a,b,c,d
run over the integers satisfying
ad-bc=1
. Taking
\gamma
to be the identity gives the bound
|c\tau+d|\leq 1
which can be satisfied by only a finite number of integer pairs
(c,d)
. We construct
\gamma
as a product of translations by
T
and inversions by
S
using an iterative process that terminates. Starting from an arbitrary
\tau\in{\cal H}
which is not in
F
, apply a translation
T^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}
to map
\tau\to\tau^{\prime}=T^{\alpha}\tau
into the vertical strip
\tau^{\prime}\in\{|{\rm Re\,}(\tau)|\leq 1/2\}
. Under this transformation, the value of the imaginary part is unchanged
{\rm Im\,}(\tau^{\prime})={\rm Im\,}(\tau)
. If
\tau^{\prime}\in F
, we are done. If
\tau^{\prime}\not\in F
then it must satisfy
|\tau^{\prime}|<1
. Its image under
S
given by
\tau^{\prime\prime}=S\tau^{\prime}
has
|\tau^{\prime\prime}|>1
as well as
{\rm Im\,}(\tau^{\prime\prime})>{\rm Im\,}(\tau^{\prime})={\rm Im\,}(\tau)
in view of the fact that
c=1
and
d=0
for
S
and that
|\tau^{\prime}|<1
. If this image is in
F
we are done, and if not, we repeat the process. Since only a finite number of pairs
(c,d)
satisfy the bound
|c\tau+d|\leq 1
, and the iterative process strictly increases the value of
{\rm Im\,}(\gamma\tau)
at each inversion
S
, the process must terminate, which completes the proof.
