The standard fundamental domain
F
for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
in
{\cal H}
; its images
T(F),S(F)
,
TS(F)
; the orbifold points
i,\rho,\rho^{\prime}
in
F
; and the cusp at
i\infty
.

3.2.3

Elliptic points

Every point
\tau\in{\cal H}
is invariant under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=\{\pm I\}
subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. An elliptic point is a point in
{\cal H}
that is invariant under a subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
that is larger than
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. We shall now show that the only elliptic points in
F
are
i,\rho,
and
\rho^{\prime}=\rho+1
, where
\rho^{3}=1
with
{\rm Im\,}(\rho)>0
. Specifically, the point
i
is invariant under the subgroup
\{\pm I,\pm S\}
of order four. The point
\rho
is invariant under the subgroup
\{\pm I,\pm(ST),\pm(ST)^{2}\}
of order six, while the point
\rho^{\prime}
is invariant under the subgroup
\{\pm I,\pm(TS),\pm(TS)^{2}\}
, also of order six.

To prove the assertion we consider a point
\tau_{\gamma}\in{\cal H}
that is invariant under
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle\gamma\tau_{\gamma}=\tau_{\gamma}\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(3.2.8)

and therefore satisfies the quadratic equation
c\tau_{\gamma}^{2}-(a-d)\tau_{\gamma}-b=0
subject to
{\rm Im\,}(\tau_{\gamma})>0
. For
c=0
there are no solutions that satisfy both conditions, while for
c\not=0
we may assume
c>0
without loss of generality. The point
\tau_{\gamma}
lies in
{\cal H}
provided
|a+d|<2
in which case
\tau_{\gamma}
is given by the solution with positive imaginary part,

\displaystyle\tau_{\gamma}={a-d\over 2c}+i{\sqrt{4-(a+d)^{2}}\over 2c}

(3.2.9)

A transformation
\gamma\to\alpha\gamma\alpha^{-1}
with
\alpha\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
maps
\tau_{\gamma}\to\alpha\tau_{\gamma}
and a suitable choice of
\alpha
maps
\tau_{\gamma}
to the standard fundamental domain
F
for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The choice
\tau_{\gamma}\in F
imposes the condition
{\rm Im\,}(\tau_{\gamma})\geq{\rm Im\,}(\rho)=\sqrt{3}/2
which requires
(a+d)^{2}+3c^{2}\leq 4
. Its solutions are
c=1
and either
a+d=0
or
a+d=\pm 1
. By a suitable translation of
\tau_{\gamma}
to the fundamental domain
F
we may set
a,d\in\{0,1\}
corresponding to the following matrices,

\displaystyle S=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\cr 1&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 57.81621ptST=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\cr 1&1\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 57.81621ptTS=\left(\begin{matrix}1&-1\cr 1&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(3.2.10)

for which
\tau_{S}=i
and
\tau_{ST}=\rho
and
\tau_{TS}=\rho^{\prime}
. Since we have
S^{2}=(ST)^{3}=-I
, their orders are 4 and 6 respectively. These are all the elliptic points of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

3.2.4

Cusps

Topologically,
F
is a sphere with one puncture at
\tau=i\infty
, referred to as the cusp of the fundamental domain of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The cusp does not belong to
{\cal H}
. It is invariant under the infinite Borel subgroup of translations and sign reversal,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\infty}=\{\pm T^{n},~n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}

(3.2.11)

Under
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}
, the cusp in
F
is mapped to the cusp of the corresponding fundamental domain
\gamma F
. Under an arbitrary
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
with
c\not=0
, the cusp is mapped to a rational number, and more precisely,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\,i\infty={\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{i\infty\}

(3.2.12)

so that each rational number is a cusp of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
in the corresponding fundamental domain.

3.3

Modular functions, modular forms, and cusp forms

We shall now define modular functions, modular forms, and cusp forms. Let
f(\tau)
be a meromorphic function on the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
, which obeys the following transformation law for some integer
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and for all
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(3.3.1)

Since
f
is a periodic function under
\tau\to\tau+1
, it has a Fourier expansion given by,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{n}\,q^{n}\hskip 72.26999ptq=e^{2\pi i\tau}

(3.3.2)

1.

f
is a modular function if
f
is meromorphic at
i\infty
, namely has only a finite number of non-vanishing Fourier coefficients
a_{n}
with negative
n
;

2.

f
is a modular form of weight
k
if it is holomorphic in
{\cal H}
and at
i\infty
, namely its Fourier coefficients
a_{n}
vanish for all negative
n
, and thus
f
is finite at
i\infty
;

3.

f
is a cusp form of weight
k
if it is a modular form of weight
k
and vanishes at
i\infty
.

3.3.1

The ring of modular forms

The space
{\cal M}_{k}
of modular forms of weight
k
for the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is a vector space over
{\mathbb{C}}
. Since the product of two modular forms of weight
k
and
\ell
is a modular form of weight
k+\ell
, the space of modular forms of arbitrary weight forms a ring
{\cal M}
graded by the weight,

\displaystyle{\cal M}=\bigoplus_{k}{\cal M}_{k}\hskip 72.26999pt{\cal M}_{k}{\cal M}_{\ell}\subset{\cal M}_{k+\ell}

(3.3.3)

The ratio of two modular forms, in general, will not be a modular form, as dividing by a cusp form will produce an object that is not holomorphic at
\infty
. In the next few subsections, we shall show that there are no modular forms of negative weight and that
{\cal M}
is a polynomial ring generated by the modular forms
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
.

3.4

Modular forms as differential forms

It is useful to view modular forms as
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant differential forms. Using the following transformation rule for
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
for the differential of
\tau
,

\displaystyle d(\gamma\tau)={d\tau\over(c\tau+d)^{2}}

(3.4.1)

we see that a modular form
f
of weight
k
is naturally mapped to a meromorphic differential form of
weight
({k\over 2},0)
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}=f(\tau)(d\tau)^{k\over 2}

(3.4.2)

which is strictly invariant under
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. This correspondence will be very useful in both mathematics and physics applications. The mismatch by a factor of 2 between the modular weight
k
of
f
and the weight
{k\over 2}
of the associated differential form
\mathfrak{f}
is conventional.

3.5

Eisenstein series

Eisenstein series
G_{k}
were already encountered when studying elliptic functions in (
2.4.5
). In this subsection, we shall show that they are modular forms of weight
k
and obtain their Fourier series decomposition. In the next subsection we shall show that they actually generate the complete polynomial ring of all modular forms.

We begin by considering the Eisenstein series defined in (
2.4.5
),
4
4

4

Throughout, the prime superscript on the sum instructs us to omit the term with
m=n=0
.

\displaystyle G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=G_{k}(\Lambda)=\sum_{\omega\in\Lambda^{\prime}}{1\over\omega^{k}}=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\prime}{1\over(m\omega_{1}+n\omega_{2})^{k}}

(3.5.1)

The sums that define
G_{k}
are absolutely convergent for
k\geq 3
, and vanish for odd
k
in view of the lattice symmetry
\Lambda=-\Lambda
. The resulting
G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations of the periods
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
since
G_{k}
depends only on the lattice
\Lambda
and not on the specific periods chosen to represent
\Lambda
. The functions
G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
are also manifestly homogeneous in the periods of degree
-k
. In summary, under an arbitrary transformation
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, which maps
\Lambda
to
\Lambda
and the periods
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
into periods
\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}
given in terms of
\gamma
in (
3.1.1
), and an arbitrary complex scaling factor
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\omega_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle G_{k}(\lambda\omega_{1},\lambda\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{-k}G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

(3.5.2)

Scaling allows us to define a function of
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
on the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
by factoring out a power of
\omega_{1}
or, equivalently, by setting
\omega_{1}=1
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=G_{k}(1,\tau)=\omega_{1}^{k}\,G_{k}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\prime}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{k}}

(3.5.3)

Combining the lattice automorphism and the scaling transformation of (
3.5
) with the definition of
G_{k}
in (
3.5.3
) gives the modular transformation law for
G_{k}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\gamma\tau)=\left({\omega_{1}^{\prime}\over\omega_{1}}\right)^{k}G_{k}(\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}G_{k}(\tau)

(3.5.4)

This transformation may also be inferred directly from the sum in (
3.5.3
). Thus,
G_{k}(\tau)
is a modular function. To show that it is a modular form we study its Fourier decomposition.

3.5.1

Fourier decomposition of Eisenstein series

Since the Eisenstein series
G_{k}(\tau)
is a holomorphic function for
\tau\in{\cal H}
as well as a periodic function of
\tau
with period 1 it admits a Fourier expansion of the form,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{\nu}(k)\,q^{\nu}\hskip 72.26999ptq=e^{2\pi i\tau}

(3.5.5)

To evaluate the Fourier series, we decompose the double sum of (
3.5.3
) that defines
G_{k}(\tau)
by isolating the contributions from
n=0
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=\sum_{m\not=0}{1\over m^{k}}+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{k}}

(3.5.6)

As
\tau
approaches the cusp
\tau\to i\infty
, the double sum in (
3.5.6
) tends to zero.
As a result, all expansion coefficients
a_{\nu}(k)
vanish for
\nu<0
and the first sum in (
3.5.6
) gives

a_{0}(k)
,

\displaystyle a_{0}(k)=2\zeta(k)\hskip 72.26999pt4\leq k\in 2{\mathbb{N}}

(3.5.7)

Thus,
G_{k}(\tau)
has a finite limit at the cusp and is a modular form of weight
k
.
To evaluate the double sum in (
3.5.6
), we use the following formula,

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{1\over z+m}=-i\pi{1+e^{2\pi iz}\over 1-e^{2\pi iz}}=-i\pi-2\pi i\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}e^{2\pi i\ell z}

(3.5.8)

which may be established by using the fact that both sides are meromorphic in
z
with simple poles of residue 1 at all integers and are periodic with period 1. Actually, we shall need the derivative of order
k-1
of this formula,

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{1\over(z+m)^{k}}={(2\pi i)^{k}\over\Gamma(k)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\ell^{k-1}\,e^{2\pi i\ell z}

(3.5.9)

Setting
z=n\tau
and including the sum over
n
, we obtain the expression for the second sum in (
3.5.6
). Putting all together gives the following Fourier series decomposition for
G_{k}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=2\zeta(k)+2{(2\pi i)^{k}\over\Gamma(k)}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\ell^{k-1}q^{n\ell}

(3.5.10)

Changing summation variables from
(n,\ell)
to
(N,\ell)
with
N=n\ell
, the sum over
\ell
for given
N
may be rearranged in terms of the sums of divisors functions, defined for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
by,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha}(N)=\sum_{\ell|N}\ell^{\alpha}

(3.5.11)

The sum is over all positive divisors
\ell
of
N
, including the divisors
1
and
N
. In terms of sums of divisor functions, we obtain our final expression for the Fourier series of
G_{k}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=2\zeta(k)+2{(2\pi i)^{k}\over\Gamma(k)}\,\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}(N)q^{N}

(3.5.12)

Factoring out
2\zeta(k)
, and using the relation between Bernoulli numbers and
\zeta(k)
for
k
even

(2\pi i)^{k}B_{k}=-2k!\zeta(k)
we obtain the normalized Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{k}
, defined by,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)={G_{k}(\tau)\over 2\zeta(k)}=1+\nu_{k}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}(N)\,q^{N}\hskip 50.58878pt\nu_{k}=-{2k\over B_{k}}

(3.5.13)

With this normalization, we manifestly have
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)\to 1
as
\tau\to i\infty
for any even
k\geq 4
.
The first few values for
\nu_{k}
are given as follows,

\displaystyle\nu_{4}=240\hskip 25.29494pt\nu_{6}=-504\hskip 25.29494pt\nu_{8}=480\hskip 25.29494pt\nu_{10}=-264\hskip 25.29494pt\nu_{12}={65520\over 691}

(3.5.14)

In particular, the modular forms
g_{2},g_{3},G_{4},G_{6}
, and the discriminant
\Delta
of (
2.4.14
) for the lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
are given in terms of the normalized Eisenstein series by,

\displaystyle g_{2}(\tau)=~\,60\,G_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{4\pi^{4}\over 3}{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle g_{3}(\tau)=140\,G_{6}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{8\pi^{6}\over 27}{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau)=g_{2}^{3}-27g_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{(2\pi)^{12}\over 12^{3}}\big({\sf E}_{4}(\tau)^{3}-{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)^{2}\big)

(3.5.15)

Since
{\sf E}_{k}(i\infty)=1
the discriminant vanishes at the cusp and we have
\Delta(\tau)=(2\pi)^{12}q+{\cal O}(q^{2})
. While the functions
G_{k}
are modular forms of weight
k
for even
k\geq 4
, the discriminant
\Delta
is a cusp form of weight 12.

3.5.2

Poincaré series representation

One construction of elliptic functions for a lattice
\Lambda
is through the method of images, exhibited in all generality in (
2.3.4
) and shown in detail for the case of the Weierstrass function in (
2.4.1
). One may view the construction by the method of images as performing a sum over the Abelian group
\Lambda
under which every elliptic function is invariant.

To obtain modular functions and modular forms, which are defined to be invariant or covariant under the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, an analogous method is available through the so-called Poincaré series. This method may be applied to obtain meromorphic modular forms, as will be the case here, but also non-holomorphic modular functions and forms, as will be the case in subsection
4.2
and section
9
. To construct a modular form by a Poincaré series one starts with a meromorphic function
g
or a meromorphic differential form
\mathfrak{g}
of weight
k
and aims to sum over its images under the modular group. In many practical cases, however, the function
g
or the form
\mathfrak{g}
is invariant under a “stability subgroup”
\Gamma_{{\rm stab}}
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. When this subgroup has an infinite number of elements, as will often be the case, the sum over all images under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
would diverge. The Poincaré series is instead defined by summing over the images under the cosets
\Gamma_{{\rm stab}}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. For the case of a meromorphic function
g
we obtain a meromorphic modular function
f
given by the Poincaré series,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{\gamma\,\in\,\Gamma_{{\rm stab}}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})}g(\gamma\tau)

(3.5.16)

The function
g
is usually referred to as the seed of the Poincaré series. The corresponding formula defines the Poincaré series for a modular form
\mathfrak{f}
from a seed
\mathfrak{g}
. As noted originally by Poincaré, the seed function for a given modular function is not unique. In particular, the Poincaré sum of certain functions may vanish.

By way of example, we may take the seed to be simply
\mathfrak{g}=(d\tau)^{k}
for some positive integer
k
. This seed is invariant under
-I\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
because
\tau
is invariant, as well as under the following Borel subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
since
d\tau
is invariant under shifts
\tau\to\tau+b
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\infty}=\left\{{\pm}\left(\begin{matrix}1&b\cr 0&1\end{matrix}\right),\,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}\right\}

(3.5.17)

so that
\Gamma_{{\rm stab}}=\Gamma_{\infty}
.
The remaining Poincaré series for even
k\geq 4
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})}{(d\tau)^{k}\over(c\tau+d)^{k}}={1\over 2}\sum_{{c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\atop\gcd(c,d)=1}}{(dz)^{k}\over(c\tau+d)^{k}}

(3.5.18)

The restriction
\gcd(c,d)=1
implicitly assumes that
(c,d)\not=(0,0)
. The form
\mathfrak{f}
is closely related to the Eisenstein series defined in (
3.5.3
), as may be seen by factoring out the greatest common divisor
p=\gcd(m,n)\in{\mathbb{N}}
from the summation variables by parametrizing them as follows
(m,n)=(pd,pc)
with
\gcd(c,d)=1
,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=\sum^{\prime}_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{k}}=\zeta(k)\sum_{{c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\atop\gcd(c,d)=1}}{1\over(c\tau+d)^{k}}

(3.5.19)

so that
\mathfrak{f}=2\zeta(k)G_{k}(\tau)(dz)^{k}
for
k>2
even.

3.5.3

The polynomial ring of Eisenstein series

The Eisenstein series
G_{2k}
is a modular form of weight
2k
for any integer
k\geq 2
. We shall now show that the Eisenstein series
G_{2k}
may be expressed as a polynomial in
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
with rational coefficients, so that the vector space generated by the Eisenstein series
G_{2k}
is a polynomial ring generated by
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
.

To show this, we differentiate the differential equation for the Weierstrass
\wp
-function
(\wp^{\prime})^{2}=4\wp^{3}-60G_{4}\wp-140G_{6}
to obtain the following second order differential equation,

\displaystyle\wp^{\prime\prime}=6\wp^{2}-30G_{4}

(3.5.20)

which is even in
z
. The Laurent expansion for
\wp
, obtained earlier in (
2.4.1
) for an arbitrary lattice
\Lambda
, may be expressed as follows for the lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\wp(z)={1\over z^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2k+1)G_{2k+2}\,z^{2k}

(3.5.21)

Substituting this expression and its double derivative into (
3.5.20
), we see that the terms in
z^{-4}
,
z^{0}
, and
z^{2}
automatically match. For the remaining terms, after some simplifications, we obtain the following recursion relation for
k\geq 4
,

\displaystyle G_{2k}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-3}{3(2\ell+1)(2k-2\ell-3)\over(k-3)(2k-1)(2k+1)}G_{2\ell+2}G_{2k-2\ell-2}

(3.5.22)

which shows recursively that
G_{2k}
for
k\geq 4
is a polynomial in
G_{2m+4}
with
0\leq m\leq k-4
, of homogeneous weight
2k
. Therefore,
G_{2k}
for
k\geq 4
is a polynomial in
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
. For example, to the lowest few orders we have,

\displaystyle G_{8}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{3}{7}\,G_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle G_{14}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{30}{143}\,G_{4}^{2}\,G_{6}

\displaystyle G_{10}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{5}{11}\,G_{4}\,G_{6}

\displaystyle G_{16}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{9}{221}\,G_{4}^{4}+\tfrac{300}{2431}\,G_{4}\,G_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle G_{12}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{18}{143}\,G_{4}^{3}+\tfrac{25}{143}\,G_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle G_{18}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{3915}{46189}\,G_{4}^{3}\,G_{6}+\tfrac{125}{4199}\,G_{6}^{3}

(3.5.23)

Thus, the subspace of the graded ring of all modular forms
{\cal M}
that is generated by the Eisenstein series
G_{2k}
for all
k\geq 4
is a polynomial ring generated by
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
. While the modular forms
G_{4}
,
G_{6}
,
G_{8}
,
G_{10}
, and
G_{14}
are the only Eisenstein series of weight
4,6,8,10
, and
14
respectively, we see that at weight 12 there are two independent modular forms, namely
G_{4}^{3}
and
G_{6}^{2}
, whose difference involves the discriminant
\Delta
.

Equivalently, we may express
G_{2k}
in terms of the normalized Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{2k}
, to obtain the following formula,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2k}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-3}{6(2\ell+1)(2k-2\ell-3)\zeta(2\ell+2)\zeta(2k-2\ell-2)\over(k-3)(2k-1)(2k+1)\zeta(2k)}\,{\sf E}_{2\ell+2}\,{\sf E}_{2k-2\ell-2}

(3.5.24)

in terms of which the relations (
3.5.3
) become,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{8}

\displaystyle={\sf E}_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{14}

\displaystyle={\sf E}_{4}^{2}{\sf E}_{6}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{10}

\displaystyle={\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{16}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1617}{3617}\,{\sf E}_{4}^{4}+\tfrac{2000}{3617}\,{\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{12}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{441}{691}\,{\sf E}_{4}^{3}+\tfrac{250}{691}\,{\sf E}_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{18}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{38367}{43867}\,{\sf E}_{4}^{3}{\sf E}_{6}+\tfrac{5500}{43867}\,{\sf E}_{6}^{3}

(3.5.25)

These relations, together with the Fourier series expansions of the
{\sf E}_{k}
in (
3.5.13
), imply an infinite number of identities between sums of divisor functions. The identities corresponding to the relations
{\sf E}_{8}={\sf E}_{4}^{2}
and
{\sf E}_{10}={\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}
are given as follows,

\displaystyle\sigma_{7}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}(N)+120\sum_{M=1}^{N-1}\sigma_{3}(N-M)\sigma_{3}(M)

\displaystyle 11\,\sigma_{9}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 21\sigma_{5}(N)-10\sigma_{3}(N)+5040\sum_{M=1}^{N-1}\sigma_{3}(N-M)\sigma_{5}(M)

(3.5.26)

More generally, the divisor functions
\sigma_{2k-1}
may be expressed as polynomials in
\sigma_{3}
and
\sigma_{5}
.

3.6

Dimension and generators of the ring of modular forms

To obtain the dimension of the space
{\cal M}_{k}
of all modular forms of weight
k
, we begin by proving the following theorem on modular functions of weight
k
. We shall denote by
{\rm ord}_{f}(p)
the order of a zero (counted positively) or pole (counted negatively) of
f
at a point
p
, and by
{\rm ord}_{f}(\infty)
the exponent of the leading term in the
q
-expansion of
f(\tau)
. Meromorphicity of modular functions guarantees that
{\rm ord}_{f}(p)
are integers, including for the cusp
p=i\infty
and for the elliptic points
p=i
and
p=\rho=e^{2\pi i/3}
.

Theorem 3.2

A non-zero modular function
f
of weight
k
satisfies the
valence formula
,

\displaystyle{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)+{1\over 2}{\rm ord}_{f}(i)+{1\over 3}{\rm ord}_{f}(\rho)+\sum_{p\in F\setminus\{i\infty,i,\rho\}}{\rm ord}_{f}(p)={k\over 12}

(3.6.1)

where
F
is the standard fundamental domain of the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
given in (
3.2.6
).

To prove the theorem, we count the number of zeros and poles of
f
by integrating the logarithmic derivative
f^{\prime}/f
over a suitable contour. Doing so presents complications at the points
i\infty,i,
and
\rho
, so we regularize the domain
F
to a domain
F_{\rm reg}
in which we cut off
F
near those points, as shown in figure
7
. The small circular arcs near
i,\rho
, and
\rho^{\prime}
have radius
1/T
. For simplicity, we shall assume that
f(\tau)
has neither zeros nor poles on
\partial F_{\rm reg}
, and refer to standard mathematics textbooks when this is not the case.

0
-{1\over 2}
{1\over 2}
F_{\rm reg}
-{1\over 2}+iT
{1\over 2}+iT
{\cal H}
i
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\rho^{\prime}
\rho

Figure 7
:
Integration contour of the regularized fundamental domain
F_{\rm reg}
.

On the one hand, the residue theorem gives,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}{1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\partial F_{\rm reg}}d\tau\,{f^{\prime}(\tau)\over f(\tau)}=\sum_{p\in F_{{\rm reg}}}{\rm ord}_{f}(p)

(3.6.2)

On the other hand, the integral along
\partial F_{{\rm reg}}
may be evaluated by using the relations under modular transformations that identify the different line segments of
\partial F_{{\rm reg}}
with one another. First, the contributions from the two vertical edges cancel one another, as their integrals are opposite. Second, we evaluate the contributions at the point
i\infty
by using the dominant behavior at
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)=T\gg 1
given by
f(\tau)\sim q^{{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)}=e^{2\pi i\tau\,{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)}
to obtain the contribution
-{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)
. The minus sign arises from the orientation of the contour. Analogous contributions are collected at
i
, but are counted with a factor of
{1\over 2}
since the integration contour is only half a circle. Similarly, at
\rho
, the contribution is only
{1\over 6}
of a full circle, but there are identical contributions from
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
which accounts for the final factor of
{1\over 3}
.
The above contributions account for the left side of (
3.6.1
). Finally, we collect the contribution along the
|\tau|=1
segments of the contour. The contributions to the left and to the right of
i
are related to one another by the transformation
S:\tau\to-1/\tau
, which acts on
f(\tau)
by
f(S\tau)=\tau^{k}f(\tau)
, so that taking the differential of the logarithm we have,

\displaystyle d(S\tau){f^{\prime}(S\tau)\over f(S\tau)}=d\tau{f^{\prime}(\tau)\over f(\tau)}+k{d\tau\over\tau}

(3.6.3)

The integration arc goes from
\tau=\rho
to
\tau=i
and spans
{1\over 12}
of a full circle, reproducing the right side of the (
3.6.1
) and thereby completing the proof of the theorem.

3.6.1

Dimension and generators of
{\cal M}_{k}

While Theorem
3.2
applies to any modular function of weight
k
, whether holomorphic or not, we shall now specialize to modular forms, which are holomorphic, and use Theorem
3.2
to compute the dimensions of the spaces
{\cal M}_{k}
. Modular forms
f(\tau)
can have no poles, so that
{\rm ord}_{f}(p)\geq 0
, including at the points
p=i\infty,i,\rho
.

Theorem 3.3

Theorem
3.2
implies the following properties of
{\cal M}_{k}
:

1.

For
k
odd,
k<0
, and
k=2
there are no modular forms, i.e.
\dim{\cal M}_{k}=0
;

2.

For
k
even and
k\geq 0
the space
{\cal M}_{k}
is finite-dimensional;

3.

For
k=0,4,6,8,10,14
, we have
\dim{\cal M}_{k}=1
and
{\cal M}_{k}={\mathbb{C}}G_{k}
;

4.

The subspace
{\cal S}_{k}
of weight
k
cusp forms is empty for
k<12
, while
{\cal S}_{12}={\mathbb{C}}\Delta
;

5.

For
k\geq 16
, we have
{\cal S}_{k}=\Delta{\cal M}_{k-12}
and
{\cal M}_{k}={\mathbb{C}}G_{k}\oplus{\cal S}_{k}
.

For low weight these results are consistent with the results of (
3.5.3
). Moreover, the theorem implies that
{\cal M}
is precisely the polynomial ring freely generated by
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
.

To prove Theorem
3.3
, we use the valence formula (
3.6.1
) of Theorem
3.2
. Considering the relation (
3.6.1
) multiplied by 12, and then mod 2, we see that there can be no solutions for
k
odd.
Since modular forms have
{\rm ord}_{f}(p)\geq 0
, including at the points
p=i,\rho,
and
i\infty
, the left side of (
3.6.1
) is non-negative so that there can be no solutions to (
3.6.1
) for
k<0
. Clearly, there is also no solution when
k=2
, which concludes the proof of point 1. For
k
even and positive, there can only be a finite number of solutions since the sum on the left of (
3.6.1
) is over positive or zero numbers only, proving point 2. To proceed further, it is helpful first to prove two auxiliary statements,

\displaystyle G_{4}(\rho)=G_{6}(i)=0

(3.6.4)

They follows from inspection of the sums,

\displaystyle G_{4}(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(m,n)}^{\prime}{1\over(m\,\rho^{3}+n\,\rho)^{4}}={1\over\rho^{4}}\sum_{(m,n)}^{\prime}{1\over(m\,\rho^{2}+n)^{4}}=\rho^{2}G_{4}(\rho)

\displaystyle G_{6}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(m,n)}^{\prime}{1\over(m\,i^{4}+n\,i)^{6}}={1\over i^{6}}\sum_{(m,n)}^{\prime}{1\over(m\,i^{3}+n)^{6}}=-G_{6}(i)

(3.6.5)

To recast the second sum on the first line, we use
m\rho^{2}+n=m(-1-\rho)+n=n-m-m\rho
so that the sum over
(m,n)
gives
G_{4}(\rho)
. To recast the second sum on the second line, we use
m\,i^{3}+n=-im+n
, so that the sum over
(m,n)
gives
G_{6}(i)
.

By inspection of (
3.6.1
), we must have
{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)=0
for
k=4,6,8,10
since the right side of (
3.6.1
) is less than 1, as well as for
k=14
since the remaining
1/6
cannot be accounted for by non-vanishing
{\rm ord}_{f}(i)
or
{\rm ord}_{f}(\rho)
. Therefore, the solutions to (
3.6.1
) for these values of
k
are unique and respectively given by
({\rm ord}_{f}(i),{\rm ord}_{f}(\rho))=(0,1),\,(1,0),\,(0,2),\,(1,1),\,(1,2)
. They are generated by
G_{4}
,
G_{6}
,
G_{8}\sim G_{4}^{2}
,
G_{10}\sim G_{4}G_{6}
,
G_{14}\sim G_{4}^{2}G_{6}
, which proves point 3. To prove point 4, we note that a cusp form has
{\rm ord}_{f}(i\infty)\geq 1
, which requires
k\geq 12
, and uniqueness for
k=12
. Finally for
k\geq 16
, the solutions manifestly satisfy the recursion relation
{\cal S}_{k}=\Delta{\cal M}_{k-12}
and
{\cal M}_{k}={\mathbb{C}}G_{k}\oplus{\cal S}_{k}
by inspecting (
3.6.1
), proving point 5.

3.7

Modular functions and the
j
-function

Having classified all modular forms under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of weight
k
, we now classify all meromorphic modular functions of weight
k
. Given an arbitrary meromorphic modular function of weight
k
, we may always divide by a modular form of weight
k
, such as
G_{k}
, to obtain a meromorphic modular function of weight 0. Thus, it will suffice to understand the space of modular functions of weight 0. Sums, products, and ratios of modular functions of weight 0 produce again modular functions of weight 0, so that the corresponding space forms the field of modular functions of weight 0. We seek to obtain a complete description of this space.

Clearly, the only holomorphic modular function of weight 0 is constant in
\tau
.
We begin by constructing a modular function with a single pole at the cusp and no other poles. Normalizing the residue of the pole to unity, we define the famous
j
-function by,

\displaystyle j(\tau)={12^{3}g_{2}^{3}\over\Delta}={12^{3}\,{\sf E}_{4}^{3}\over{\sf E}_{4}^{3}-{\sf E}_{6}^{2}}={1\over q}+{\cal O}(q^{0})

(3.7.1)

Inspecting (
3.6.1
), we have
k=0
since we are dealing with a modular function of weight zero;
{\rm ord}_{\infty}(j)=-1
since
j
has a simple pole at
\infty
;
{\rm ord}_{i}(j)=0
since
{\sf E}_{4}(i)\not=0
; and
{\rm ord}_{\rho}(j)=3
since
j
has a triple zero at
\rho
in view of the identity
G_{4}(\rho)={\sf E}_{4}(\rho)=0
established in (
3.6.4
). These values indeed satisfy (
3.6.1
), and we have,
5
5

5

The first 19 orders in the
q
-expansion of
j
are as follows:
j(\tau)=q^{-1}+744+196884\,q+21493760\,q^{2}+864299970\,q^{3}+20245856256\,q^{4}+333202640600\,q^{5}+4252023300096\,q^{6}+44656994071935\,q^{7}+401490886656000\,q^{8}+3176440229784420\,q^{9}+22567393309593600\,q^{10}+146211911499519294\,q^{11}+874313719685775360\,q^{12}+4872010111798142520\,q^{13}+25497827389410525184\,q^{14}+126142916465781843075\,q^{15}+593121772421445058560\,q^{16}+2662842413150775245160\,q^{17}+{\cal O}(q^{18})
.

\displaystyle j(i\infty)=\infty\hskip 72.26999ptj(i)=1\hskip 72.26999ptj(\rho)=0

(3.7.2)

The classification of modular functions is given as follows.

Theorem 3.4

Every rational function of
j(\tau)
is a modular function of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Conversely, every modular function
f(\tau)
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of weight 0 is a rational function of
j
. In terms of the zeros
z_{n}
and poles
p_{n}
(both of which are to be counted with multiplicities, including at
i
and
\rho
), we have the explicit formula,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\prod_{n=1}^{N}{j(\tau)-j(z_{n})\over j(\tau)-j(p_{n})}

(3.7.3)

This result completes the construction of the field of modular functions of weight 0 and, combining it with our earlier general construction of modular forms of arbitrary weight, also gives the construction for all meromorphic
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-covariant functions of arbitrary weight.

3.7.1

Modular forms with prescribed zeros

A final comment is in order about modular forms with prescribed zeros. From (
3.6.1
) we see that a zero at a regular point in
F
requires
k\geq 12
, and the addition of one regular zero requires shifting
k\to k+12
. To construct, for example, the modular forms of weight 12 with one zero at an arbitrary point
p\in F\setminus\{i\infty,i,\rho\}
, we take a linear combination of the two modular forms available, namely
{\sf E}_{4}^{3}
and
{\sf E}_{6}^{2}
, and arrange the coefficients so that a zero emerges at
p
,

\displaystyle f_{p}(\tau)=f_{0}\Big({\sf E}_{4}(\tau)^{3}{\sf E}_{6}(p)^{2}-{\sf E}_{4}(p)^{3}{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)^{2}\Big)

(3.7.4)

The normalization factor
f_{0}
may be fixed by evaluation at any other point, including
i\infty
. One proceeds analogously for modular forms with more zeros at prescribed points.

3.8

Modular transformations of Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions

The Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions are subject to more delicate modular transformation laws, which we shall now derive. Recall the definition of the basic
\vartheta
-function,

\displaystyle\vartheta(z|\tau)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{i\pi\tau n^{2}+2\pi inz}

(3.8.1)

Setting
z=0
and
\tau=it
for
t
real and positive, we may apply the Poisson summation formula (
2.1.11
) to obtain the transformation rule for
S\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})=(-i\tau)^{{1\over 2}}\,\vartheta(0|\tau)

(3.8.2)

Although originally derived for
t
real, the formula remains valid for
\tau\in{\cal H}
provided we choose the proper branch cut for the square root. Under the modular transformation
T
, however, the
\vartheta
-function does not transform into itself, but instead we have,

\displaystyle\vartheta(z|\tau+1)=\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)

(3.8.3)

where
\vartheta_{4}
is a
\vartheta
-function with half-integer characteristics defined in (
2.6.2
). The set of four
\vartheta
-functions with half characteristics transforms into itself, and we have,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(\tfrac{z}{\tau}|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

\displaystyle=-i(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}e^{i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tfrac{z}{\tau}|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

\displaystyle=(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}e^{i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tfrac{z}{\tau}|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

\displaystyle=(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}e^{i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(z|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{3}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tfrac{z}{\tau}|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

\displaystyle=(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}e^{i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)

(3.8.4)

where
\varepsilon=e^{2\pi i/8}
. One may collect these transformation laws in a single expression for each transformation, valid for all half-characteristics,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{matrix}\right](z|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\pi\alpha(1+\alpha)}\,\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\cr\beta+\alpha+{1\over 2}\cr\end{matrix}\right](z|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{matrix}\right]\left(\tfrac{z}{\tau}\Big|-\tfrac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}\,e^{2\pi i\alpha\beta+i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\,\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\beta\cr-\alpha\cr\end{matrix}\right](z|\tau)

(3.8.5)

Combining the two, one finds that under a generic element
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
the
\vartheta_{1}
function transforms as follows,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z^{\prime}|\tau^{\prime})=\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)(c\tau+d)^{1\over 2}\,e^{\pi icz^{2}/(c\tau+d)}\,\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)

(3.8.6)

from which the transformation of the
\vartheta
-function with arbitrary characteristic may be deduced using (
2.6.7
), and we find,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha+{1\over 2}\cr\beta+{1\over 2}\cr\end{matrix}\right]\left(z^{\prime}|\gamma\tau\right)=\varepsilon(\alpha,\beta\,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma)(c\tau+d)^{1\over 2}e^{\pi icz^{2}/(c\tau+d)}\,\,\vartheta\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha^{\prime}+{1\over 2}\cr\beta^{\prime}+{1\over 2}\cr\end{matrix}\right](z|\tau)

(3.8.7)

where,

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\alpha,\beta\,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma)=\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)\,e^{\pi i\left\{\alpha(\beta+1)-\alpha^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}+1)\right\}}

(3.8.8)

and
z
and the characteristics transform as follows,

\displaystyle z^{\prime}={z\over c\tau+d}\hskip 50.58878pt\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha^{\prime}\cr\beta^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}a\alpha+c\beta\cr b\alpha+d\beta\end{matrix}\right]\hskip 50.58878pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)

(3.8.9)

Finally,
\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)
and
\varepsilon(\alpha,\beta\,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma)
are both eight roots of unity and
\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)
is independent of the characteristics
\alpha,\beta
. Its evaluation is in terms of the generalized Legendre symbol and will be given in subsection
3.9
on the Dedekind
\eta
function.

Setting
z=0
in the theta functions gives so-called theta-constants, whose modular transformation laws are special cases of the general transformation laws presented above. None of these are modular forms under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, though we shall see later that they are modular forms under certain congruence subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Furthermore, the derivative
\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0|\tau)
is closely related to the discriminant
\Delta
and various sums of eight powers of
\vartheta_{2},\vartheta_{3}
, and
\vartheta_{4}
are modular forms. We now discuss both of these constructions in turn.

3.8.1

The discriminant
\Delta
:
\vartheta
-constants and product formula

The modular transformation law for
\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)
is readily obtained from (
3.8
) and is given below along with its eighth power,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau+1)=\varepsilon\,\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})=(-i\tau)^{{3\over 2}}\,\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau+1)^{8}=\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)^{8}

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|-\tfrac{1}{\tau})^{8}=(-i\tau)^{12}\,\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)^{8}

(3.8.10)

While
\vartheta^{\prime}_{1}
itself is not a modular form, its eight power is a modular form of weight 12, and therefore must be a linear combination of
{\sf E}_{4}^{3}
and
{\sf E}_{6}^{2}
. To obtain a precise relation, we appeal to the infinite product formula for
\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)
given in (
2.7
), and find the following expression,

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\prime}_{1}(0|\tau)=\pi\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)=2\pi q^{1\over 8}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})^{3}

(3.8.11)

This expression indeed fails to be a modular form in view of the
q^{1\over 8}
prefactor. However, its eight power produces a factor of
q
which is not only consistent with being a genuine modular form, but also shows that it is a cusp form proportional to the discriminant
\Delta
. The constant of proportionality is easily fixed by matching the term of order
q
in the Fourier expansion of both. As a result, we find not only a relation between
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)^{8}
and the discriminant
\Delta
but also a product formula for
\Delta
,

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau)=(2\pi)^{4}\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)^{8}=(2\pi)^{12}q\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})^{24}

(3.8.12)

The product formula for the discriminant shows that
\Delta(\tau)
is nowhere vanishing for
\tau\in{\cal H}
, and has a simple zero in
q
at the cusp.

The Fourier coefficients
\tau(n)
of
\Delta
, defined by,

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau)=(2\pi)^{12}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tau(n)q^{n}

(3.8.13)

are referred to as the Ramanujan
\tau
-function, and have special arithmetic significance.
The lowest orders are given as follows,

\displaystyle\tau(1)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\tau(4)

\displaystyle=-1472

\displaystyle\tau(7)

\displaystyle=-16744

\displaystyle\tau(10)

\displaystyle=-115920

\displaystyle\tau(2)

\displaystyle=-24

\displaystyle\tau(5)

\displaystyle=4830

\displaystyle\tau(8)

\displaystyle=84480

\displaystyle\tau(11)

\displaystyle=534612

\displaystyle\tau(3)

\displaystyle=252

\displaystyle\tau(6)

\displaystyle=-6048

\displaystyle\tau(9)

\displaystyle=-113643

\displaystyle\tau(12)

\displaystyle=-370944

(3.8.14)

The relation
1728\Delta=(2\pi)^{12}(E_{4}^{3}-E_{6}^{2})
along with
E_{4}^{2}=E_{8}
allows us to express the
\tau
-function in terms of sums of divisor functions as follows,

\displaystyle\tau(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\left(\tfrac{200}{3}\,\sigma_{3}(m)\sigma_{7}(n-m)-147\,\sigma_{5}(m)\sigma_{5}(N-m)\right)

(3.8.15)

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{5}{36}\,\sigma_{3}(n)+\tfrac{7}{12}\,\sigma_{5}(n)+\tfrac{5}{18}\,\sigma_{7}(n)

Eliminating
\sigma_{7}
in terms of
\sigma_{3}
using the first formula in (
3.5.3
) confirms that
\tau(n)
may be expressed solely in terms of
\sigma_{3}
and
\sigma_{5}
. Ramanujan conjectured that
\tau(n)
is a multiplicative arithmetic function, which satisfies,

\displaystyle\tau(mn)=\tau(m)\tau(n)\hskip 72.26999pt\gcd(m,n)=1

(3.8.16)

where
\gcd(m,n)
stands for the greatest common divisor of
m
and
n
.
Ramanujan’s function is, however, not completely multiplicative (which would mean that the first equation in (
3.8.16
) would hold for arbitrary
m,n
, not necessarily relatively prime) and instead satisfies,

\displaystyle\tau(m)\tau(n)=\sum_{d\,|\,{\gcd}(m,n)}d^{11}\tau\left({mn\over d^{2}}\right)

(3.8.17)

Both conjectures on
\tau(n)
were proven by Mordell in 1917 using the theory of Hecke operators applied to the cusp form
\Delta(\tau)
, and we shall provide this proof in section
10
.

3.8.2

Sums of eight powers

Another way to construct a modular form using the
\vartheta
-constants is by sums of eighth powers. The modular transformation properties show right away that the following sums,

\displaystyle T_{4k}(\tau)=\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{8k}+\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{8k}+\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{8k}

(3.8.18)

are modular forms of weight
4k
. For
k=1,2
, the corresponding spaces of modular forms are one-dimensional, so that we must have
T_{4}=2{\sf E}_{4}
and
T_{8}=2{\sf E}_{8}=2{\sf E}_{4}^{2}
by matching asymptotics at the cusp. In particular, we may express the modular
j
-function in terms of eighth powers of
\vartheta
-constants,

\displaystyle j(\tau)=1728{g_{2}(\tau)^{3}\over\Delta(\tau)}=32\,{\big(\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)^{8}\big)^{3}\over\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)^{8}\,\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)^{8}\,\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)^{8}}

(3.8.19)

It may be verified directly from the asymptotics of the
\vartheta
-constants at the cusp that
j(q)
has a simple pole at the cusp with unit residue
j(q)\sim q^{-1}
.

3.9

The Dedekind
\eta
-function

The Dedekind
\eta
-function is defined as follows,

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)=q^{1\over 24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})

(3.9.1)

It is not a modular form of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but its 24th power is a modular form of weight 12 and related to the discriminant as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau)=(2\pi)^{12}\eta(\tau)^{24}

(3.9.2)

Under the generators
T
and
S
of the modular group,
\eta
transforms as follows,

\displaystyle\eta(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i/24}\,\eta(\tau)

\displaystyle\eta(-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-i\tau)^{1\over 2}\,\eta(\tau)

(3.9.3)

The first relation may be read off from the definition of
\eta
, while the second relation may be derived first for
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
using Poisson resummation, and then be extended to
\tau\in{\cal H}
.
6
6

6

The square root is defined via the argument function
\arg(\tau)
for
\tau\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, in terms of which
\tau=|\tau|\exp(i\arg(\tau))
and
-\pi\leq\arg(\tau)<\pi
by
\tau^{1\over 2}=|\tau|^{1\over 2}\exp(i\arg(\tau)/2)
(see for example
[
7
]
). This definition is compatible with the fact that for
\tau=it
with
t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
we have
\eta(it),\eta(i/t)>0
.

The transformation of
\eta(\tau)
under an arbitrary
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
may be expressed as follows,

\displaystyle\eta(\gamma\tau)=\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)\,(c\tau+d)^{1\over 2}\eta(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)

(3.9.4)

with
\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)^{24}=1
, or
\varepsilon:SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{24}
. The function
\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)
depends on
\gamma
, but not on
\tau
. Its values may be determined from the transformation rules given in (
3.9
). Note that the actions of the transformations
\gamma
and
-\gamma
coincide on
\tau
but differ on the square root and on
\varepsilon_{\eta}
giving, in particular, the value
\varepsilon_{\eta}(-I)=(-1)^{1\over 2}
. In view of this property,
\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)
is not a proper homomorphism of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{24}
but instead is referred to as a multiplier system. Special values may be deduced from the basic transformation laws (
3.9
),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\eta}(T^{n})=e^{2\pi in/24}\hskip 43.36243pt\varepsilon_{\eta}(S)=e^{-2\pi i/8}\hskip 43.36243pt\varepsilon_{\eta}(-I)=e^{2\pi i/4}=i

(3.9.5)

In particular, if
\gamma
is such that either
c>0
, or
c=0
and
d>0
, then
\varepsilon(-\gamma)=i\varepsilon(\gamma)
.
The Petersson formula for
\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)
for a
\gamma
with either
c>0
, or
c=0
and
d>0
is given as follows, (see
[
7
]
)

\displaystyle c\hbox{ odd: }~

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)=(d|c)\exp\left\{\tfrac{2\pi i}{24}\big((a+d)c+bd(1-c^{2})-3c\big)\right\}

(3.9.6)

\displaystyle c\hbox{ even: }

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)={\rm sign}(d)(c|d)\exp\left\{\tfrac{2\pi i}{24}\big((a+d)c+bd(1-c^{2})+3d+3-3cd\big)\right\}

We recall from appendix
A
, and in particular (
A.4.3
), that the Jacobi symbol
(n|N)
for odd denominator
N
(which is always the case here) with decomposition into distinct primes
p_{i}
given by
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
is defined by
(n|N)=\prod_{i}(n|p_{i})
where
(n|p_{i})
is the Legendre symbol for an odd prime
p_{i}
. The definition is extended to include
n=0
by
(0|1)=(0|-1)=1
and negative
N
by
(n|N)=(n|-N)
.

Knowledge of
\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)
allows us to produce a formula for the multiplier
\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)
encountered in the modular transformation law of the
\vartheta
-functions in (
3.8.7
) by using the relation,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)=2\pi\eta(\tau)^{3}

(3.9.7)

which is obtained by combining (
3.8.11
) with the definition of the
\eta
-functions. The modular transformation of the left side may be derived from (
3.8.6
) while the modular transformation of the right side is given by (
3.9.4
), and we obtain,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\vartheta}(\gamma)=\varepsilon_{\eta}(\gamma)^{3}

(3.9.8)

consistent with the fact that
\varepsilon_{\vartheta}
is an 8-th root of unity while
\varepsilon_{\eta}
is a 24-th root of unity.

Finally, we note that the fourth power
\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}(\gamma)
no longer suffers from the subtleties of sign reversal of
\gamma
and the square root, and is a genuine homomorphism
\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}:SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{6}
with
\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}(-I)=1
and
\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})=\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}(\gamma_{1})\varepsilon_{\eta}^{4}(\gamma_{2})
.
