Bibliographical notes

The books by Apostol
[
8
]
and Lang
[
9
]
and the lecture notes by Zagier
[
10
]
offer classic introductions to the subject of modular forms. The books by Shimura
[
11
]
and Koblitz
[
12
]
provide advanced and more abstract presentations aimed at arithmetic. A marvelously clear and detailed presentation, to which we shall refer often in these notes, is given in the fairly recent treatise by Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
. A general perspective from the point of view of automorphic functions is given in Bateman
[
14
]
and Iwaniec
[
15
]
. A detailed exposition of identities involving the Dedekind
\eta
-function and the role they play in the theory of modular forms may be found in the book by Köhler
[
7
]
. Presentations with an emphasis on
L
-functions and
q
-series expansions may be found in the books by Bump
[
16
]
and Ono
[
17
]
, respectively.

4

Variants of modular forms

In this section, we discuss a variety of objects that are closely related to modular forms, including quasi-modular forms, almost-holomorphic modular forms, non-holomorphic Eisenstein series, Maass forms, mock modular forms, and quantum modular forms. Other generalizations, including modular forms for congruence subgroups, vector-valued modular forms, and modular graph forms, will be introduced in sections
7
,
8
, and
9
respectively.

4.1

Quasi-modular and almost-holomorphic modular forms

Quasi-modular forms and almost-holomorphic modular forms are generalizations of the modular forms introduced earlier, which are obtained by relaxing certain parts of the definition of modular forms. We begin by reviewing the most famous of these objects, referred to as
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
and
{\sf E}^{*}_{2}(\tau)
respectively, before proceeding to the definition and presentation of the general properties of quasi-modular forms and almost-holomorphic modular forms.

4.1.1

{\sf E}_{2}
and
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}

We introduced the holomorphic Eisenstein series
G_{k}
in (
2.4.5
) and (
3.5.1
), or equivalently
{\sf E}_{k}
in (
3.5.13
), which are given for even
k\geq 4
in terms of absolutely convergent sums over a lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
. For
k=2
, the corresponding sum,

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\Lambda^{\prime}}{1\over\omega^{2}}=\sum_{{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\atop(m,n)\not=(0,0)}}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{2}}

(4.1.1)

fails to be absolutely convergent. Instead, it is conditionally convergent and its value will depend on how the infinite sums are arranged. Physicists would say that the sums need to be “regularized”. There may be one or several choices that are deemed “natural” because they preserve one property or another of modular forms.

One choice of regularization is to adopt the Eisenstein summation which consists in cutting off the infinite sums in a symmetrical way, as was already used in (
2.2.2
),

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\lim_{M\to\infty}\,{1\over 2\zeta(2)}\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\sum_{{m=-M\atop(m,n)\not=(0,0)}}^{M}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{2}}

(4.1.2)

For finite
M
and
N
the sum is holomorphic in
\tau
and the limit
M,N\to\infty
produces a finite holomorphic function
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
. However,
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
fails to transform as a modular form under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations. This is not surprising as Theorem
3.3
tells us there are no modular forms of weight 2. Instead the transformation law of
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
is as follows,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{2}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+{12\over 2\pi i}\,c(c\tau+d)

(4.1.3)

To show this, we decompose the sum of (
4.1.2
) into contributions from
n=0
and
n\not=0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)=1+\lim_{N\to\infty}\lim_{M\to\infty}{1\over\zeta(2)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=-M}^{M}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{2}}

(4.1.4)

and perform the sum over
m
using (
3.5.9
) in the convergent limit
M\to\infty
. The remaining sum over
n
admits a finite limit as
N\to\infty
and evaluates as follows,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)=1-24\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\ell\,e^{2\pi i\ell n\tau}=1-24\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(\ell)q^{\ell}

(4.1.5)

Alternatively, the sum over
n
in (
4.1.5
) may be performed first and the remaining sum over
\ell
may be rearranged in terms of the discriminant
\Delta(\tau)
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)={1\over 2\pi i}\,\partial_{\tau}\ln\Delta(\tau)

(4.1.6)

Using the modular transformation law of the weight 12 modular form
\Delta
under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
we deduce the transformation law of
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
by differentiating
\ln(\Delta)
to obtain (
4.1.3
).
Remarkable relations to the Weierstrass
\zeta
and
\wp
functions include the following,

\displaystyle\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2}|\tau)={\pi^{2}\over 6}\,{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\wp(z|\tau)=-\partial_{z}^{2}\ln\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)-{\pi^{2}\over 3}\,{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)

(4.1.7)

The latter demonstrates how
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
acts as a modular connection to guarantee the proper modular transformation of
\wp
given that of the
\vartheta_{1}
-function.

Another choice of regularization was introduced by Siegel by defining the following sum,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}{1\over 2\zeta(2)}\sum_{\omega\in\Lambda^{\prime}}{1\over\omega^{2}\,|\omega|^{\varepsilon}}

(4.1.8)

Finite
\varepsilon>0
renders the sum over
\Lambda^{\prime}
absolutely convergent and the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
turns out to be finite.
The result is a weight 2 modular form under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{2}{\sf E}^{*}_{2}(\tau)

(4.1.9)

However,
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
fails to be holomorphic in
\tau
. The regularization factor
|\omega|^{\varepsilon}
introduces non-holomorphic behavior for
\varepsilon>0
which leaves a non-holomorphic remnant as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
7
7

7

Throughout, we shall use the notation
\tau=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}
with
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)={\sf E}_{2}(\tau)-{3\over\pi\tau_{2}}

(4.1.10)

where the holomorphic
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
was given in (
4.1.6
). One verifies that the modular transformation laws of
{\sf E}_{2}
and
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}
map into one another under this relation.

In summary, while the Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{k}
for
k\geq 4
are holomorphic modular forms of weight
k
, the regularization of the infinite sum for
k=2
allows for two “natural” choices: one is holomorphic but not modular, while the other is modular but not holomorphic. One cannot enforce both modularity and holomorphicity simultaneously. The incompatibility between holomorphicity and modular invariance may be viewed as perhaps the first example of what physicists refer to as an anomaly. Eisenstein’s result dates back to before 1850 !

4.1.2

The rings of almost-holomorphic modular and quasi-modular forms

A function
f:{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is an almost-holomorphic form of weight
k
with respect to the full modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
if it transforms as follows under
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)

(4.1.11)

and is a polynomial in
\tau_{2}^{-1}
whose coefficients
f_{n}(q)
are holomorphic functions of
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}f_{n}(q)\tau_{2}^{-n}

(4.1.12)

By this definition, every modular form of weight
k
is an almost-holomorphic modular form of weight
k
, and
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
is an almost-holomorphic modular form of weight 2. Clearly, any linear combination of almost-holomorphic modular forms of weight
k
is an almost-holomorphic modular form of weight
k
, and the product of two almost-holomorphic modular forms of weights
k
and
\ell
is an almost-holomorphic modular form of weight
k+\ell
, so that the space of almost-holomorphic forms a ring graded by the weight.

A function
g:{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a quasi-modular form of weight
k
if it is the holomorphic part
f_{0}(q)
of some almost-holomorphic form
f
of weight
k
, as defined above. By this definition, every modular form of weight
k
is a quasi-modular form of weight
k
, and
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
is a quasi-modular form of weight 2. The space of quasi-modular forms is a ring graded by the weight. More precisely, we have the following theorem, presented here without proof.

Theorem 4.1

The space of almost-holomorphic modular forms is a polynomial ring, graded by the weight
k
, and generated by
{\sf E}_{2}^{*},{\sf E}_{4}
, and
{\sf E}_{6}
. The space of quasi-modular forms is a polynomial ring, graded by the weight
k
, and generated by
{\sf E}_{2},{\sf E}_{4}
, and
{\sf E}_{6}
.

4.1.3

Differential equations for quasi-modular forms

The inhomogeneous nature of the transformation law of
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
suggests that
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
may be viewed geometrically as a connection in the space of modular forms
{\cal M}
. The connection
E_{2}
produces a covariant derivative
D_{k}
acting on
{\cal M}_{k}
,

\displaystyle D_{k}:{\cal M}_{k}\to{\cal M}_{k+2}\hskip 72.26999ptD_{k}={1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}-{k\over 12}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)

(4.1.13)

To show this, we use the fact that a modular form
f
of weight
k
, namely
f\in{\cal M}_{k}
, and its
\tau
-derivative
f^{\prime}
satisfy the following transformation rules,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\gamma\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\tau+d)^{k+2}f^{\prime}(\tau)+kc(c\tau+d)^{k+1}f(\tau)

(4.1.14)

Combining (
4.1.3
) with the above relation, and the definition of
D_{k}
, we obtain,

\displaystyle D_{k}f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k+2}D_{k}f(\tau)

(4.1.15)

so that
D_{k}f
is a modular form of weight
k+2
, as announced. The Leibnitz formula for the product of two modular forms
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of respective weights
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
is given as follows,

\displaystyle D_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\left(f_{1}f_{2}\right)=\left(D_{k_{1}}f_{1}\right)f_{2}+f_{1}\left(D_{k_{2}}f_{2}\right)

(4.1.16)

When the space
{\cal M}_{k+2}
is one-dimensional, one easily derives a differential equation for modular forms of weight
k
. The precise normalizations may be deduced from the asymptotic behavior at the cusp. For example, we have,

\displaystyle D_{4}\,{\sf E}_{4}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{3}{\sf E}_{6}

\displaystyle D_{8}\,{\sf E}_{8}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{2}{3}{\sf E}_{10}

\displaystyle D_{12}\,\Delta

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D_{6}\,{\sf E}_{6}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\sf E}_{8}

\displaystyle D_{12}\,{\sf E}_{12}

\displaystyle=-{\sf E}_{14}

(4.1.17)

Equivalent expressions may be obtained by using
{\sf E}_{8}={\sf E}_{4}^{2}
,
{\sf E}_{10}={\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}
, and
{\sf E}_{14}={\sf E}_{4}^{2}{\sf E}_{6}
together with Leibnitz’s rule. The derivative of
{\sf E}_{10}
is, however, more involved since the space
{\cal M}_{12}
is two-dimensional.

Given that
{\sf E}_{2}
may naturally be interpreted as a connection in
{\cal M}_{k}
there should be a natural notion of curvature. The associated curvature should transform tensorially, namely it should be a modular form, and should be obtained from the derivative and the square of the connection. Given the transformation law (
4.1.3
) for
{\sf E}_{2}
, we obtain the transformations for its derivative with respect to
\tau
and its square,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\tau+d)^{4}{\sf E}_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)+2c(c\tau+d)^{3}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+\tfrac{12c^{2}}{2\pi i}(c\tau+d)^{2}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\tau+d)^{4}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)^{2}+\tfrac{24}{2\pi i}\,c(c\tau+d)^{3}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+\left(\tfrac{12c}{2\pi i}\right)^{2}(c\tau+d)^{2}

(4.1.18)

As a result, the following combination transforms as a modular form of weight 4,

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}{\sf E}_{2}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})-{1\over 12}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau^{\prime})^{2}=(c\tau+d)^{4}\left({1\over 2\pi i}{\sf E}_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)-{1\over 12}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)^{2}\right)

(4.1.19)

Since this combination is both holomorphic in
\tau
and a modular form of weight 4, it must belong to
{\cal M}_{4}
. But by Theorem
3.3
, the space
{\cal M}_{4}
is dimension 1 and is generated by
{\sf E}_{4}
. Matching the value at
q=0
, we obtain the relation,

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)-{1\over 12}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)^{2}=-{1\over 12}{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)

(4.1.20)

It is natural to interpret
{\sf E}_{4}
as the curvature, given indeed by a linear combination of the derivative and the square of
{\sf E}_{2}
.
We note that the coefficient multiplying the square is half of what one would have expected if the derivative had been applied to a modular form of weight 2. This is as usual when obtaining the curvature from the connection.

4.2

Non-holomorphic Eisenstein series

We next introduce non-holomorphic Eisenstein series, which may be viewed as generalizing the Riemann
\zeta
-function, modular forms, and almost-holomorphic modular forms. Given a lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}+\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
, a non-holomorphic Eisenstein series may be defined by the following lattice sum,
8
8

8

The notation
E_{s}(\tau)
used for the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series should not be confused with the notation
{\sf E}_{s}(\tau)
used for the holomorphic Eisenstein series.
or so-called Kronecker-Eisenstein sum,

\displaystyle E_{s}(\tau)=\sum_{\omega\in\Lambda^{\prime}}{\tau_{2}^{s}\over\pi^{s}|\omega|^{2s}}=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\prime}{\tau_{2}^{s}\over\pi^{s}|m+n\tau|^{2s}}

(4.2.1)

where
\tau_{2}={\rm Im\,}(\tau)
and the prime on the sum indicates that the contribution
m=n=0
is to be omitted from the sum. The sum is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
. The lattice sum definition of
E_{s}(\tau)
may be used to show that
E_{s}
is modular invariant,

\displaystyle E_{s}(\gamma\tau)=E_{s}(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(4.2.2)

It may also be used to factor the non-trivial common divisors of
m
and
n
in the double sum,

\displaystyle E_{s}(\tau)=\zeta(2s)\sum_{{c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}\atop\gcd(c,d)=1}}^{\prime}{\tau_{2}^{s}\over\pi^{s}|c\tau+d|^{2s}}

(4.2.3)

where
\zeta(2s)
is the Riemann
\zeta
-function and the remaining sum is over pairs
(c,d)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
that have no common divisors strictly greater than 1. This expression in turn allows us to write
E_{s}(\tau)
as a Poincaré series over the coset
\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
as follows,

\displaystyle E_{s}(\tau)={\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})}\left({\rm Im\,}\gamma\tau\right)^{s}

(4.2.4)

This relation implies that
E_{s}(\tau)
vanishes for all
\tau
whenever
2s
is a zero of the Riemann
\zeta
-function, including at its zeros on the negative real axis, as well as at its non-trivial zeros when
{\rm Re\,}(4s)=1
.
Finally, the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
is an eigenfunction of the Laplace equation,

\displaystyle\Delta E_{s}(\tau)=s(s-1)E_{s}(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}=\tau_{2}^{2}(\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2})

(4.2.5)

as may be verified by applying the Laplace operator term-wise in the sum of (
4.2.1
).

4.2.1

Analytic continuation of
E_{s}(\tau)

While
E_{s}(\tau)
is defined by the series (
4.2.1
) for
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
, it may be analytically continued in
s
throughout the complex
s
-plane to produce a meromorphic function, just as was possible for the Riemann
\zeta
-function. To exhibit the analytic structure of
E_{s}(\tau)
in
s
, we use the following integral representation for the summands,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\left(\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi t|m+n\tau|^{2}/\tau_{2}}-1\right)

(4.2.6)

The subtraction of
1
eliminates the zero mode. The non-analyticity in
s
arises from the small
t
regime. As we did in the case of the Riemann
\zeta
-function, we split the integral at
t=1
and Poisson re-sum the integrand for
0<t<1
in both
m
and
n
to obtain,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\left(\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi t|m+n\tau|^{2}/\tau_{2}}-1\right)

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\left({1\over t}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi|m+n\tau|^{2}/(t\tau_{2})}-1\right)

Next, we change variable in the second integral
t\to 1/t
, collect the resulting integrals for
t\in[1,\infty[
,
and analytically continue a trivial remaining integral. The result is as follows,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)=-{1\over s(1-s)}+\int_{1}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,\Big(t^{s}+t^{1-s}\Big)\left(\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi t|m+n\tau|^{2}/\tau_{2}}-1\right)

(4.2.8)

The first term on the right side is meromorphic in
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, while the integral is manifestly holomorphic throughout
{\mathbb{C}}
. As a result,
E_{s}(\tau)
admits a meromorphic analytic continuation to
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
with a simple pole at
s=1
with residue 1. Moreover, the expression is manifestly invariant under
s\to 1-s
which implies the functional equation,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)=\Gamma(1-s)E_{1-s}(\tau)

(4.2.9)

In particular, the functional relation requires
E_{s}(\tau)=0
whenever
s=-1,-2,\cdots
in view of the fact that the right side is finite for these values and that
\Gamma(s)
on the left side is infinite. This observation reproduces our earlier observation that
E_{s}(\tau)=0
whenever
2s
is a zero of the Riemann
\zeta
-function.

4.2.2

Fourier series of
E_{s}(\tau)

The Fourier series of
E_{s}(\tau)
for all
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
may be derived from the integral representation in (
4.2.6
),
this time by Poisson re-summation in the variable
m
only, using the formula,

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi t|m+n\tau|^{2}/\tau_{2}}=\sqrt{{\tau_{2}\over t}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{2\pi imn\tau_{1}}\,e^{-\pi m^{2}\tau_{2}/t-\pi n^{2}t\tau_{2}}

(4.2.10)

In the mathematics literature this procedure is referred to as the Chowla-Selberg method.
Substituting this result into (
4.2.6
) and splitting the sum over
m,n
into contributions for
mn\not=0
and
mn=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)=A_{s}(\tau)+B_{s}(\tau)

(4.2.11)

where,

\displaystyle A_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sum_{m\not=0}\sum_{n\not=0}e^{2\pi imn\tau_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s-{1\over 2}}\,e^{-\pi\tau_{2}(m^{2}/t+n^{2}t)}

\displaystyle B_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\left(-1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{{\tau_{2}\over t}}\,e^{-\pi n^{2}t\tau_{2}}+\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sqrt{{\tau_{2}\over t}}\,e^{-\pi m^{2}\tau_{2}/t}\right)

(4.2.12)

The integral
A_{s}(\tau)
is manifestly holomorphic in
s
throughout
{\mathbb{C}}
, and may be computed by performing the
t
-integral in terms of a
K
-Bessel function,

\displaystyle A_{s}(\tau)=2\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sum_{m,n\not=0}\left|{m\over n}\right|^{s-{1\over 2}}e^{2\pi imn\tau_{1}}K_{s-{1\over 2}}(2\pi\tau_{2}|mn|)

(4.2.13)

The integral
B_{s}(\tau)
may be computed by Poisson re-summing the
m
-sum in the integrand,

\displaystyle B_{s}(\tau)=\sum_{n\not=0}\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\left(\sqrt{{\tau_{2}\over t}}\,e^{-\pi n^{2}t\tau_{2}}+e^{-\pi n^{2}t/\tau_{2}}\right)

(4.2.14)

which is readily evaluated in terms of the Riemann
\zeta
-function or associated
\xi
-function,

\displaystyle B_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Gamma(s)\pi^{-s}\tau_{2}^{s}\zeta(2s)+2\Gamma(s-\tfrac{1}{2})\pi^{{1\over 2}-s}\tau_{2}^{1-s}\zeta(2s-1)

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{2}^{s}\xi(2s)+\tau_{2}^{1-s}\xi(2s-1)

where
\xi(s)
is the function computed in (
2.1.16
). We may now verify the functional relation (
4.2.9
) directly in terms of the transformation properties of
A_{s}(\tau)
and
B_{s}(\tau)
. For
A_{s}(\tau)
this is manifest upon using the symmetry under interchange of
m
and
n
, while for
B_{s}(\tau)
it is a consequence of the relation
\xi(1-s)=\xi(s)
. Assembling both contributions, and recasting
A_{s}(\tau)
in terms of a summation over a single variable
N=mn
, we obtain the celebrated Fourier series decomposition for
E_{s}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Gamma(s)\pi^{-s}\,\zeta(2s)\,\tau_{2}^{s}+2\Gamma(s-\tfrac{1}{2})\pi^{{1\over 2}-s}\,\zeta(2s-1)\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}

(4.2.16)

\displaystyle+4\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sum_{N\not=0}|N|^{{1\over 2}-s}\sigma_{2s-1}(|N|)\,e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}K_{s-{1\over 2}}(2\pi\tau_{2}|N|)

where
\sigma_{\alpha}(n)
is defined to be the sum
\sigma_{\alpha}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d^{\alpha}
of positive divisors
d
of
n
. One verifies that each Fourier mode, including the power-behaved terms on the first line, individually satisfy the Laplace eigenvalue equation, as required by the fact that
\Delta
commutes with the translation operator
\partial_{\tau_{1}}
.

For integer
s=k
the Bessel function in the Fourier series becomes a spherical Bessel function
K_{k-{1\over 2}}(x)
, which is a combination of an exponential in
x
and a polynomial in
1/x
. As a result, the Fourier series of
E_{k}(\tau)
simplifies and may be recast as follows,

\displaystyle E_{k}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-)^{k+1}{B_{2k}\over(2k)!}\,y^{k}+{4\,(2k-3)!\,\zeta(2k-1)\over(k-2)!\,(k-1)!\,y^{k-1}}

(4.2.17)

\displaystyle\quad+{2\over(k-1)!}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}N^{k-1}\sigma_{1-2k}(N)P_{k}(Ny)\left(q^{N}+\bar{q}^{N}\right)

where
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
and
y=4\pi\tau_{2}
. Furthermore,
B_{2k}
are the Bernoulli numbers and
P_{k}(x)
is a polynomial in
1/x
given by,

\displaystyle P_{k}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}{(k+m-1)!\over m!\,(k-m-1)!\,x^{m}}

(4.2.18)

Non-holomorphic Eisenstein series with integer
s
will play a crucial role in the theory of modular graph functions and forms, to be developed in section
9
.

4.3

Maass forms

The non-holomorphic Eisenstein series discussed in the previous subsection are special examples of a more general class of non-holomorphic objects known as Maass forms. A non-holomorphic function
f(\tau)
is called a Maass form if it is
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant, satisfies a Laplace eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle\Delta f(\tau)=\lambda f(\tau)\hskip 57.81621pt\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}=\tau_{2}^{2}(\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2})

(4.3.1)

for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and has at most polynomial growth at the cusps. Without loss of generality, we will write
\lambda=s(s-1)
and denote the space of such Maass forms by
{\cal N}(s)
, where
{\cal N}(s)={\cal N}(1-s)
. From (
4.2.5
) we see that the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
are elements of
{\cal N}(s)
. Indeed, below we will see that for generic
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series are the only elements of
{\cal N}(s)
.

The Fourier expansions of generic Maass forms admit surprisingly simple closed form expressions. In general, non-holomorphic modular forms admit Fourier expansions in separate powers of
q
and
\bar{q}
, of the type,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{M,N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\widetilde{c}_{M,N}(\tau_{2})q^{M}\bar{q}^{N}

(4.3.2)

with
\widetilde{c}_{M,N}(\tau_{2})
being functions of
\tau_{2}
but not of
\tau_{1}
, since the latter would violate invariance under
T:\tau\rightarrow\tau+1
. It is often useful to absorb all
\tau_{2}
dependence, including that of
q
and
\bar{q}
, into the Fourier coefficients, giving an alternative form for the Fourier expansion as,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}c_{N}(\tau_{2})\,e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}

(4.3.3)

We will now see that, for Maass forms, the coefficients
c_{N}(\tau_{2})
can be fixed uniquely, up to overall constants. Indeed, inverting the Fourier expansion gives,

\displaystyle c_{N}(\tau_{2})=\int_{0}^{1}d\tau_{1}\,e^{-2\pi iN\tau_{1}}\,f(\tau)

(4.3.4)

and we may make use of the eigenvalue equation to obtain,

\displaystyle c_{N}(\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over s(s-1)}\int_{0}^{1}d\tau_{1}\,e^{-2\pi iN\tau_{1}}\,\Delta f(\tau)

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\tau_{2}^{2}\over s(s-1)}\Big[(2\pi iN)^{2}c_{N}(\tau_{2})+\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2}c_{N}(\tau_{2})\Big]

This gives a second order differential equation for the coefficients
c_{N}(\tau_{2})
,

\displaystyle\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2}c_{N}(\tau_{2})=\Big(s(s-1)+(2\pi N\tau_{2})^{2}\Big)c_{N}(\tau_{2})

(4.3.6)

For
N=0
the general solution is of the form,

\displaystyle c_{0}(\tau_{2})=a\,\tau_{2}^{s}+b\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}

(4.3.7)

For
N\neq 0
we obtain a modified Bessel equation with general solutions
I_{s-1/2}
and
K_{s-1/2}
. Discarding the solutions
I_{s-1/2}
since they grow exponentially at the cusp, we obtain,

\displaystyle c_{N}(\tau_{2})=\alpha_{N}\,\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,K_{s-1/2}\left(2\pi\tau_{2}|N|\right)\hskip 36.135ptN\neq 0

(4.3.8)

We conclude that a general element of
{\cal N}(s)
has a Fourier expansion of the form,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=a\,\tau_{2}^{s}+b\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}+\sum_{N\neq 0}\alpha_{N}\,\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}K_{s-1/2}\left(2\pi\tau_{2}|N|\right)

(4.3.9)

Note that the Fourier expansions of the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series were obtained already in (
4.2.16
), and match with the above result upon substituting,

\displaystyle a={2\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\hskip 28.90755ptb={2\Gamma(s-{1\over 2})\pi^{{1\over 2}-s}{\zeta(2s-1)}\over\Gamma(s)}\hskip 28.90755pt\alpha_{N}={4|N|^{{1\over 2}-s}\sigma_{2s-1}(|N|)\over\Gamma(s)}

(4.3.10)

The constant part of the Fourier expansion, i.e. the term
a\,\tau_{2}^{s}+b\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}
, is known as the Laurent polynomial of the Maass form. Though it may at first sight seem that the space of Laurent polynomials is two-dimensional and spanned by
a
and
b
, in fact this space is only one-dimensional. This may be shown by considering Maass forms on the truncated fundamental domain,

\displaystyle F_{L}=\left\{\tau\in{\cal H},\,\,|\tau|\geq 1,\,\,|\mathrm{Re}(\tau)|\leq{1\over 2},\,\,\tau_{2}\leq L\right\}

(4.3.11)

Indeed, consider two Maass forms
f,g\in{\cal N}(s)
with Fourier expansions

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}c_{N}(\tau_{2})e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}\hskip 43.36243ptg(\tau)=\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}d_{N}(\tau_{2})e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}

(4.3.12)

Green’s theorem on the region
F_{L}
states that if
{\partial\over\partial n}
is the normal derivative on
\partial F_{L}
and
ds
is the differential arc length, then we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial F_{L}}ds\left(f{\partial g\over\partial n}-d{\partial f\over\partial n}\right)=\int_{F_{L}}{d\tau_{1}d\tau_{2}\over\tau_{2}^{2}}(f\Delta g-g\Delta f)=0

(4.3.13)

where the last equality follows from the eigenvalue equation. The left side may be evaluated in terms of the Fourier coefficients. Noticing that modular invariance reduces the boundary integral to an integral over
\tau_{2}=L
(since the remaining boundaries of the fundamental domain give equal and opposite contributions), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial F_{L}}ds\left(f{\partial g\over\partial n}-f{\partial f\over\partial n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-{1\over 2}}^{{1\over 2}}d\tau_{1}\sum_{N,M\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left(c_{N}(L)d^{\prime}_{M}(L)-c^{\prime}_{N}(L)d_{M}(L)\right)e^{2\pi i(N+M)\tau_{1}}

(4.3.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left[c_{N}(L)d^{\prime}_{-N}(L)-c^{\prime}_{N}(L)d_{-N}(L)\right]

where the derivative is defined by,

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{N}(L)={\partial c_{N}(\tau_{2})\over\partial\tau_{2}}\Big|_{\tau_{2}=L}

(4.3.15)

and similarly for
d^{\prime}_{-N}(L)
. In particular, this implies that
c_{0}d^{\prime}_{0}-c^{\prime}_{0}d_{0}=0
, and if we write,

\displaystyle c_{0}(\tau_{2})=a\,\tau_{2}^{s}+c\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}\hskip 50.58878ptd_{0}(\tau_{2})=b\,\tau_{2}^{s}+d\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}

(4.3.16)

then for
s\neq{1\over 2}
we obtain
ad-bc=0
, which indeed means that the space of Laurent polynomials has dimension one. In the case that
s={1\over 2}
this space is also obviously of dimension one, since then the two factors of
\tau_{2}
have the same power.

4.3.1

The space of Maass forms

As was mentioned above, at generic values of
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
the space of Maass forms
{\cal N}(s)
consists only of non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
. More generally, at non-generic values of
s
, the space of Maass forms can include cusp forms. In the current context, cusp forms are defined to be the subset of elements of
{\cal N}(s)
such that the Laurent polynomial vanishes, that is to say
a=b=0
in (
4.3.9
). We will denote the space of cusp forms by
{\cal S}(s)
. The non-holomorphic Eisenstein series are clearly not cusp forms for any
s
. For generic values of
s
, the set
{\cal S}(s)
will be empty.

The structure of
{\cal N}(s)
can be summarized as follows:

Theorem 4.2

The space of Maass forms
{\cal N}(s)
is given by

\displaystyle{\cal N}(s)=\left\{\begin{matrix}{\mathbb{C}}E_{s}(\tau)&&\mathrm{Re}\,s>{1\over 2}\,\,\mathrm{and}\,\,s\notin[0,1]\\
{\mathbb{C}}E_{s}(\tau)\oplus{\cal S}(s)&&\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}\,\,\,\mathrm{or}\,\,\,s\in[0,1)\\
{\mathbb{C}}&&s=1\end{matrix}\right.

(4.3.17)

The proof of this theorem follows almost immediately from the fact, proven above, that the space of Laurent polynomials has dimension 1. This means that for any
f(\tau)\in{\cal N}(s)
, there exists a constant
c
such that the combination
f(\tau)-cE(\tau,s)
has trivial Laurent polynomial, i.e. is a cusp form.

To complete the proof, we need only prove that the space of cusp forms is empty for all values of
s
which satisfy neither
\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}
nor
s\in[0,1)
. The non-existence for
s=1
is immediate: in this case the cusp form would be harmonic, i.e. satisfy a Laplace eigenvalue equation with zero eigenvalue, and hence would have to vanish by the maximum modulus principle. More generally we may argue as follows. Above we have seen that it is possible to find a constant
c
such that for any
f(\tau)\in{\cal N}(s)
, the combination
h(\tau):=f(\tau)-cE_{s}(\tau)
is a cusp form. Since cusp forms are square-integrable over the fundamental domain
F=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}
equipped with the Poincaré measure
d^{2}\tau/\tau_{2}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,|h(\tau)|^{2}<\infty

(4.3.18)

we may manipulate the following absolutely convergent integral by using the differential equation
\Delta h=s(s-1)h
satisfied by
h
, and Green’s theorem,

\displaystyle s(s-1)\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}|h(\tau)|^{2}=\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\Delta h(\tau)\,\overline{h(\tau)}=-4\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau}\left|\partial_{\tau}h\right|^{2}

(4.3.19)

Since the right side is real and non-positive, cusp forms must have real
s(s-1)\leq 0
, which restricts us to
s\in[0,1]
or
\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}
.

In closing, let us note that the triviality of the space of cusp forms for
\mathrm{Re}\,s\neq{1\over 2}
and
s\not\in{\mathbb{R}}
implies that for these values the space
{\cal N}(s)
is one-dimensional and hence there must be a functional relation relating
E_{s}(\tau)
and
E_{1-s}(\tau)
, since both have Laplace eigenvalue
s(s-1)
. Indeed, precisely such a relation was identified in (
4.2.9
).

4.3.2

Cusp forms

We have just shown that the space
{\cal S}(s)
of cusp forms is empty unless
\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}\,\,\,\mathrm{or}\,\,\,s\in[0,1)
. Actually, one can further show that
{\cal S}(s)
vanishes for
s\in[0,1)
, and hence that all non-trivial cusp forms must exist at
\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}
and away from the real axis: we refer to
[
18
]
for the proof. Denoting
s={1\over 2}+it
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the corresponding eigenvalue is
\lambda=s(s-1)=-\left({1\over 4}+t^{2}\right)
.

For any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the space
{\cal S}\left({1\over 2}+it\right)
is finite-dimensional. To show this, begin with
M
elements
v_{1}(\tau),\dots,v_{M}(\tau)\in{\cal S}\left({1\over 2}+it\right)
. Given an additional element
\widetilde{v}(\tau)
, it is possible to choose coefficients
c_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that the linear combination,

\displaystyle v(\tau):=\widetilde{v}(\tau)-\sum_{i=1}^{M}c_{i}v_{i}(\tau)

(4.3.20)

has vanishing Fourier coefficients up to order
M
. We will now show that for
M
sufficiently large but finite, the vanishing of Fourier coefficients up to order
M
implies vanishing to all orders. In other words we will have
v(\tau)=0
exactly, and hence all elements
\widetilde{v}(\tau)\in{\cal S}\left({1\over 2}+it\right)
can be written as a linear combination of a finite number
M
of other elements.

To prove the desired result, begin by recalling that a cusp form
v(\tau)
is bounded on the upper-half plane, and hence that
|v(\tau)|
must have a maximum at some location
\tau^{(0)}=\tau_{1}^{(0)}+i\tau_{2}^{(0)}
. From the form of the Fourier expansion (
4.3.9
) and orthogonality of exponentials, we have,

\displaystyle\alpha_{N}\,\sqrt{\tau_{2}^{(0)}}\,K_{it}(2\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)}|N|)=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}d\tau_{1}\,v(\tau_{1}+i{\tau_{2}^{(0)}/2})\,e^{-2\pi iN\tau_{1}}\left({\sqrt{2}K_{it}(2\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)}|N|)\over K_{it}(\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)}|N|)}\right)

(4.3.21)

It then follows that,

\displaystyle\left|v(\tau^{(0)})\right|<2\sqrt{2}\,\left|v(\tau^{(0)})\right|\,\sum_{|N|>M}{K_{it}(2\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)}N)\over K_{it}(\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)}N)}

(4.3.22)

If
M
is sufficiently large (depending on
t
), we may estimate the Bessel functions by exponentials, giving the inequality,

\displaystyle\left|v(\tau^{(0)})\right|\leq\left|v(\tau^{(0)})\right|\,Ce^{-\pi M\tau_{2}^{(0)}}

(4.3.23)

for some proportionality constant
C
. Hence if
M\geq(\ln C)/(\pi\tau_{2}^{(0)})
then we conclude that
|v(\tau^{(0)})|=0
, and hence that
v(\tau)
is identically zero.

We have just shown that
{\cal S}\left({1\over 2}+it\right)
is finite-dimensional for any
t
. Furthermore, though we will not reproduce the proof here, it has been shown that there exist an infinite number of values of
t
for which the space of cusp forms is non-empty. However, there is no exact value of
t
for which this is known to be the case. The smallest approximate values of
t
for which cusp forms are believed to exist are,

\displaystyle t\approx\,9.53369,\,\,12.17300,\,\,13.77975,\,\,14.35850,\,\,16.13807,\,\,16.64425,\,\,17.73856,\,\dots

(4.3.24)

In particular, we see that there is only a discretum of such values.

4.3.3

Comments on the physical interpretation of the spectrum

The fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is non-compact as it has a puncture at the cusp. From the point of view of scattering theory, one may view the cusp as an asymptotic region from which a free wave in the continuous spectrum can be sent in. This wave is indeed oscillatory and of the form
\tau_{2}^{{1\over 2}+it}
for real
t
, from the first constant term in the Fourier expansion. Since the fundamental domain is otherwise bounded, the wave hits a wall at
|\tau|=1
and must be reflected out through the puncture and is asymptotically of the form
\tau_{2}^{{1\over 2}-it}
. This phenomenon is analogous to one-dimensional scattering on a semi-infinite line and explains in physical terms why the Eisenstein solutions have
E_{{1\over 2}+it}(\tau)=E_{{1\over 2}-it}(\tau)
.

The cusp forms are wave functions of bound states, as is confirmed by the fact that they decay exponentially towards the cusp. In most quantum mechanics problems, the number of bound states in the presence of a wall would be finite. But there are exceptions, such as the Coulomb problem. Here, it is the special properties of the hyperbolic metric on the fundamental domain of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
that produce an infinite number of cusp forms, or bound states, with a spectrum that rises all the way to infinity.

4.4

Spectral decomposition

We have now discussed various aspects of the spectrum of the Laplacian on the fundamental domain
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}
.
It is possible to extract from this data the following orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian:

•

Discrete series:
The constant function
v_{0}:=\sqrt{\mathrm{Area}(F)}=\sqrt{3/\pi}
(which is trivially an eigenfunction of the Laplacian) together with an orthonormal basis of cusp forms
v_{n}(\tau)
with corresponding eigenvalues
s_{n}(s_{n}-1)=-\left({1\over 4}+t_{n}^{2}\right)
for
t_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. The first few values of
t_{n}
are given in (
4.3.24
).

•

Continuous series:
The non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
with
s={1\over 2}+it
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
taking a continuum of values.

Orthogonality here is defined relative to the Petersson inner product with the Poincaré measure
d^{2}\tau/\tau_{2}^{2}
on the fundamental domain, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}f(\tau)\overline{g(\tau)}

(4.4.1)

Let us use this inner product to verify the orthogonality claimed above. Consider the inner product between a non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
and a cusp form
v_{n}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\langle E_{s}(\tau),\,v_{n}(\tau)\rangle=\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}E_{s}(\tau)\overline{v_{n}(\tau)}

(4.4.2)

This inner product converges since cusp forms have exponential decay at the cusps, whereas non-holomoprhic Eisenstein series have at most polynomial growth. For
\mathrm{Re}\,s>1
, we may proceed by using the Poincaré series for
E_{s}(\tau)
given in (
4.2.4
), which gives

\displaystyle\langle E_{s}(\tau),\,v_{n}(\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\int_{F}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})}(\mathrm{Im}\,\gamma\tau)^{s}\,\overline{v_{n}(\tau)}

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\int_{-{1\over 2}}^{1\over 2}d\tau_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{d\tau_{2}\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,\tau_{2}^{s}\,\overline{v_{n}(\tau)}

where in the second equality we have used modular invariance to unfold the integral over the fundamental domain, i.e. we have replaced the integral over
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
orbits in
F
with an integral over the strip
\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash{\cal H}
,

\displaystyle\int_{SL(2,Z)\backslash{\cal H}}~~\sum_{\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,\,\,\,\,=\,\,\,\,\,\int_{\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash{\cal H}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}

(4.4.4)

This is the analog of the unfolding trick introduced for periodic functions in section
2.1.1
.
The integral over
\tau_{1}
may now be performed and isolates the Laurent polynomial of
v_{n}(\tau)
, which is vanishing by definition. This gives the desired orthogonality for
\mathrm{Re}\,s>1
. The analogous result for
\mathrm{Re}\,s<1
may be obtained by analytic continuation.

An arbitrary square integrable modular function can now be decomposed into this orthogonal basis. We state without proof the following spectral decomposition theorem,

Theorem 4.3
(Roelcke-Selberg)

Let
v_{0}=\sqrt{3/\pi}
be the normalized constant function and
\{v_{n}(\tau)\}_{n\geq 1}
be an orthonormal basis of cusps forms. Then any square integrable modular function
f(\tau)
admits the following decomposition,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\langle f(\tau),v_{n}(\tau)\rangle\,v_{n}(\tau)+{1\over 4\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,\left\langle f(\tau),\,E_{\tfrac{1}{2}+it}(\tau)\right\rangle E_{\tfrac{1}{2}+it}(\tau)\,\,

(4.4.5)

The reader may be worried about the convergence properties of the continuous portion of this decomposition. Indeed,
E_{s}(\tau)
is not square-integrable because of its Laurent polynomial. However, when
\mathrm{Re}\,s={1\over 2}
, one can show that
|E_{s}(\tau)|\ll\sqrt{\tau_{2}}
when
\tau_{2}\rightarrow\infty
, and the extra integration over
t
in the decomposition formula saves a factor of
\log\tau_{2}
, which is sufficient to turn the right side into a square-integrable function.

4.5

Maass forms of general weight

Thus far we have focused on Maass forms of weight
(0,0)
. We now discuss Maass forms of arbitrary (integer) weight
k
, i.e. those satisfying

\displaystyle f\left({a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)\hskip 36.135pt\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(4.5.1)

The Maass property in this case must be recast in terms of a weight-
k
Laplacian

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}=4\tau_{2}^{2-k}\partial_{\tau}\tau_{2}^{k}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}=\Delta-2ik\tau_{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}

(4.5.2)

in order to get a modular covariant object.

Of particular interest are the harmonic Maass forms of weight
k
, i.e. those satisfying an eigenvalue equation
\Delta_{k}f=0
. Unlike in the case of weight 0, for non-zero weight the space of harmonic Maass forms is non-trivial.

A simple example of a weight-2 harmonic Maass form is the almost-holomorphic Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
introduced in section
4.1
. Indeed, from (
4.1.10
) and the fact that
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
is holomorphic we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}{\sf E}^{*}_{2}(\tau)=-{3\over\pi}(\Delta-4i\tau_{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}})\tau_{2}^{-1}=-{3\over\pi}(2\tau_{2}^{-1}-2\tau_{2}^{-1})=0

(4.5.3)

The space of weight-
k
harmonic Maass forms
{\cal I}_{k}
admits an interesting decomposition which will feature prominently in the subsequent section. This decomposition can be captured by the following exact sequence,

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\cal M}^{!}_{k}\rightarrow{\cal I}_{k}\rightarrow{\cal M}^{!}_{2-k}\rightarrow 0

(4.5.4)

with
{\cal M}^{!}_{k}
the space of weight-
k
modular functions. The content of this exact sequence is two-fold. First, it says that the space
{\cal M}^{!}_{k}
of weight-
k
modular functions is a subset of the space
{\cal I}_{k}
of weight-
k
harmonic Maass forms. This follows trivially from the fact that modular functions are annihilated by
\Delta_{k}
. Second, it says that the space
{\cal M}^{!}_{2-k}
of weight-
(2-k)
modular functions is a quotient of
{\cal I}_{k}
by
{\cal M}^{!}_{k}
. This follows from the fact that, given an element
f(\tau)\in{\cal I}_{k}
, the function
g(\tau):=\tau_{2}^{k}\partial_{\tau}\overline{f(\tau)}
is holomorphic and of weight
2-k
, and vanishes if and only if
f(\tau)
is in
{\cal M}^{!}_{k}
.

4.6

Mock modular forms

In section
4.1
we saw that there exist two variants of the weight-two holomorphic Eisenstein series, namely the function
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
defined in (
4.1.2
) which is holomorphic but not modular, and the function
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
defined in (
4.1.8
) which is modular but not holomorphic. The former is an example of a quasi-modular form, whereas the latter is an example of an almost-holomorphic modular form. Section
4.1.2
described an isomorphism between the sets of quasi-modular forms and almost-holomorphic modular forms.

Having introduced non-holomorphic modular forms in the previous subsections, it is natural to ask if their holomorphic parts lead to interesting analogs of quasi-modular forms. In particular, we will consider the holomorphic parts of harmonic ‘‘weak” Maass forms,
9
9

9

The adjective “weak” indicates the potential for exponential growth at the cusps; in contrast, a “Maass form” is required to have at most polynomial growth at the cusp.

which are known as mock modular forms.
10
10

10

Mock modular forms made their first appearance in a letter of Ramanujan to Hardy, in which they were referred to as
mock theta functions
. In the modern definition, a mock theta function is a
q
-series
H(q)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}q^{n}
such that for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Q}}
the series
q^{\lambda}H(q)
gives a mock modular form of weight
{1\over 2}
, with the shadow (defined below) being a weight-
3\over 2
function of the form
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\epsilon(n)nq^{|\kappa|n^{2}}
with
\kappa\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\epsilon
an odd periodic function.

Given a harmonic Maass form
F^{*}(\tau)
, we may extract its holomorphic part as follows. We begin by defining,

\displaystyle g(\tau)=\tau_{2}^{k}{\partial\over\partial\tau}\overline{F^{*}(\tau)}

(4.6.1)

By the condition that
F^{*}(\tau)
be harmonic, it follows that
g(\tau)
is holomorphic, since
\Delta_{k}=\tau_{2}^{2-k}\partial_{\tau}\tau_{2}^{k}\partial_{\overline{\tau}}
.
If one can identify a new function
g^{*}(\tau)
such that its image under
\tau_{2}^{k}\partial_{\overline{\tau}}
is
\overline{g(\tau)}
, then it is clear that,

\displaystyle F(\tau):=F^{*}(\tau)-g^{*}(\tau)

(4.6.2)

will be holomorphic. Such a function
g^{*}(\tau)
can be obtained by simply inverting the differential operator
\tau_{2}^{k}\partial_{\overline{\tau}}
, giving the expression,

\displaystyle g^{*}(\tau)=\left({i\over 2}\right)^{k-1}\int_{-\overline{\tau}}^{i\infty}dz\,\,{\overline{g(-\overline{z})}\over(z+\tau)^{k}}

(4.6.3)

known as a non-holomorphic Eichler integral. This integral is independent of the path since the integrand is holomorphic in
z
.

The function
F(\tau)
is holomorphic, but will in general fail to be modular, since
g^{*}(\tau)
is not modular. The function
g(\tau)
in terms of which
F(\tau)
is defined is referred to as the shadow, and is a weight-
(2-k)
modular function. The pair of
F(\tau)
and
g(\tau)
are often together referred to as a mock modular form, though in some contexts the function
F(\tau)
alone is given that name.

Given a harmonic Maass form
F^{*}(\tau)
, it is always possible to extract from it a unique mock modular form by taking the holomorphic part in the way described above. Conversely, given a mock modular form, it is always possible to complete it to a harmonic Maass form by taking the sum of
F(\tau)
with the function
g^{*}(\tau)
obtained from the shadow. This establishes an isomorphism between the two spaces, exactly analogous to the isomorphism between the spaces of almost-holomorphic modular forms and quasi-modular forms given in section
4.1
. The space of mock modular forms then fits into an exact sequence of the form in (
4.5.4
). By nature of it being an exact sequence, the map from the space of mock modular forms to the space of shadows (known as the shadow map) is surjective.

4.6.1

Examples of mock modular forms

We now give some concrete examples of mock modular forms.

A first example is provided by
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
. In the previous section we showed that
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
is a weight-2 harmonic Maass form, and hence by definition the holomorphic part of
{\sf E}_{2}^{*}(\tau)
, namely
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
, is a weight-2 mock modular form. The shadow
g(\tau)
in this case is simply a constant, namely
g(\tau)=-{12\over\pi}
. It is easy to check that this constant gives
g^{*}(\tau)=-{3\over\pi\tau_{2}}
, reproducing the result in (
4.1.10
). Thus we see that
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
is both quasi-modular and mock modular. This is not in general the case: quasi-modular forms are generically not mock modular. For example,
({\sf E}_{2}(\tau))^{2}
is a quasi-modular form, but fails to be mock modular since its modular completion is not a harmonic Maass form.

A second example, which appears rather unexpectedly in physics via the elliptic genus of strings propagating on a K3 manifold, is the following function,

\displaystyle F^{*}(\tau)=F(\tau)+{6\over\sqrt{i}}\int_{-\overline{\tau}}^{\infty}dz\,{\overline{\eta(-\overline{z})^{3}}\over(z+\tau)^{1/2}}

(4.6.4)

with
F(\tau)
defined as,

\displaystyle F(\tau)={1\over\eta(\tau)^{3}}\bigg(-{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+24\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r>s>0\\
r-s=1\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,2\end{subarray}}(-1)^{r}\,s\,q^{rs/2}\bigg)

(4.6.5)

It is known that
F^{*}(\tau)
is modular with weight
{1\over 2}
. Hence its holomorphic part
F(\tau)
must be a mock modular function. The shadow in this case is
g(\tau)=3\sqrt{2}\,\eta(\tau)^{3}
.

A curious fact is that the first few terms in the Fourier expansion of
F(\tau)
,

\displaystyle F(\tau)=q^{-{1\over 8}}\left(-1+45q+231q^{2}+770q^{3}+2277q^{4}+5796q^{5}+\dots\right)

(4.6.6)

give dimensions of irreducible representations of the Mathieu group
M_{24}
, a sporadic simple group of order 244,823,040. This correspondence goes under the name of Mathieu moonshine, and has been the subject of much research.

As a third and final example, we mention with a classic result of Zagier, which states that the following two functions are harmonic with
k={3\over 2}
,

\displaystyle F_{0}^{*}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\sum_{n\geq 0}H(4n)\,q^{n}+6\tau_{2}^{-1/2}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\beta(4\pi n^{2}\tau_{2})\,q^{-n^{2}}

\displaystyle F_{1}^{*}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\sum_{n>0}H(4n-1)\,q^{n-{1\over 4}}+6\tau_{2}^{-1/2}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\beta\left(4\pi(n+1/2)^{2}\tau_{2}\right)q^{-\left(n+{1\over 2}\right)^{2}}

(4.6.7)

where
\beta(t)
is an incomplete gamma function

\displaystyle\beta(t)={1\over 16\pi}\int_{1}^{\infty}du\,u^{-3/2}e^{-ut}

(4.6.8)

and
H(n)
are the Hurwitz class number of imaginary quadratic fields. The latter are defined for
n>0
to be the number of
PSL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
equivalence classes of integral binary quadratic forms of discriminant
-n
, weighted by the reciprocal of the number of their automorphisms. The first few non-zero entries are given by

\displaystyle H(3)={1\over 3}\hskip 36.135ptH(4)={1\over 2}\hskip 36.135ptH(7)=H(8)=H(11)=1

(4.6.9)

For
n=0
we have
H(0)=-{1\over 12}
and for
n<0
we have
H(n)=0
.

The holomorphic parts
F_{0,1}(\tau)
of
F^{*}_{0,1}(\tau)
are easy to read off,

\displaystyle F_{0}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\sum_{n\geq 0}H(4n)q^{n}

\displaystyle F_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\sum_{n>0}H(4n-1)q^{n-{1\over 4}}

(4.6.10)

and have corresponding shadows

\displaystyle g_{0}(\tau)=-{3\over 4\pi}\vartheta_{3}(0|2\tau)\hskip 36.135ptg_{1}(\tau)=-{3\over 4\pi}\vartheta_{2}(0|2\tau)

(4.6.11)

It is important to note that these functions are not mock modular forms in the strict sense discussed above, since under
\tau\rightarrow-1/\tau
the original harmonic functions
F^{*}_{0,1}(\tau)
are not individually invariant, but rather transform into one another as

\displaystyle\binom{F^{*}_{0}(-1/\tau)}{F^{*}_{1}(-1/\tau)}=-{(-i\tau)^{3/2}\over\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{matrix}\right)\binom{F^{*}_{0}(\tau)}{F^{*}_{1}(\tau)}

(4.6.12)

There are two ways of interpreting this fact. The first is to think of
F^{*}_{0,1}(\tau)
as forming a vector-valued harmonic Maass form. In this interpretation the functions
F_{0,1}(\tau)
likewise form a vector-valued mock modular form. Vector-valued modular forms will be discussed in more detail in section
8
.

The second approach is to notice that
F^{*}_{0}(\tau)
is invariant under
T
and
F^{*}_{1}(\tau)
is invariant under
T^{4}
. Hence we can say that
F^{*}_{0}(\tau)
individually is modular invariant under the subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
generated by
T
and
ST^{4}S
. This subgroup is denoted by
\Gamma_{0}(4)
, and is one of the so-called congruence subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, discussed in detail in section
6
. In this interpretation one would say that
F^{*}_{0}(\tau)
is a harmonic Maass form for the congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(4)
, and that
F_{0}(\tau)
is likewise a mock modular form for this subgroup. Modular forms for congruence subgroups will be discussed in detail in section
7
.

4.7

Quantum modular forms

Finally, let us briefly mention the notion of a quantum modular form.
11
11

11

As far as we are aware, there is no direct connection between the term “quantum” used here and the usual notion of “quantum” in Physics.
Unlike for standard modular forms for which the domain is the upper half-plane, quantum modular forms are defined on the space of cusps
{\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{i\infty\}
(or some subset thereof). Since this domain is a discrete space, quantum modular forms do not obey the usual analyticity properties of modular forms. Furthermore, they are not modular in the standard sense. Instead, a weight-
k
quantum modular form
f(\tau)
is defined such that for all
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, the difference

\displaystyle h_{\gamma}(\tau):=f(\tau)-\epsilon_{\gamma}(c\tau+d)^{k}f\left({a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\right)

(4.7.1)

is an analytic function on the upper half-plane. Here we have allowed for a multiplier phase
\epsilon_{\gamma}
, similar to that appearing in the transformation rules for the Dedekind
\eta
-function in section
3.9
.

It is simplest to illustrate this definition by proceeding directly to an example. Consider the following
q
-expansion,

\displaystyle F(q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-q^{i+1})

(4.7.2)

known as Kontsevich’s strange function. This function does not converge on any open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
, but is well-defined at roots of unity, i.e. when
\tau\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. We now define the closely related object,

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)=q^{1\over 24}F(q)

(4.7.3)

It follows from this definition that
\phi(\tau+1)=e^{2\pi i/24}\phi(\tau)
.

To understand the behavior under
S
, we first note that the
T
transformation just given implies
\phi(n)=e^{2\pi in/24}\phi(0)=e^{2\pi in/24}
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. On the other hand, Kontsevich and Zagier have shown that for
\tau=-{1\over n}
, one has,

\displaystyle\phi\left(-{1\over n}\right)\sim n^{3/2}e^{2\pi i{(3+n)\over 24}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\left({2\pi i\over n}\right)^{j}

(4.7.4)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, with the first few coefficients in the expansion being,

\displaystyle c_{0}=1\hskip 43.36243ptc_{1}={23\over 24}\hskip 43.36243ptc_{2}={1681\over 1152}

(4.7.5)

Using the
T
transformation, the first term above can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle n^{3/2}e^{2\pi i\over 8}\phi(n)=(-in)^{3/2}\phi(n)

(4.7.6)

and hence we conclude that the function

\displaystyle h_{S}(n):=\phi(n)+(-in)^{3/2}\phi(-1/n)

(4.7.7)

has a well-defined Taylor expansion at
\tau=0
, and so is smooth there. Indeed, the
n
-th derivative of
h_{S}(n)
at
\tau=0
is given by
c_{n}\left({2\pi i/n}\right)^{n}
. Though we do not give details here, the function
h_{S}(n)
can furthermore be extended to a real-analytic function on
{\mathbb{C}}
(except for at
\tau=0
), so
\phi(\tau)
is an example of a quantum modular form.

Bibliographical notes

[

. The spectral decomposition theorem has recently been used in a physics context in

[

A fundamental role was played by quasi-modular forms in Viazovska’s proof of the sphere packing problem in eight dimensions, namely that no packing is more dense than the one corresponding to the root lattice of
E_{8}

[

, while the generalization to sphere packing in dimension 24 was settled in
[

.

Reviews on mock modular forms include the lecture notes by Zagier

in the mathematics literature and

The third example of mock modular forms, given in (

Quantum modular forms were introduced in mathematics in
