but their role in physics remains unexplored to date, despite the word “quantum” in their name.

5

Quantum fields on a torus

In this section, we shall present some immediate applications of the theory of elliptic and modular functions to problems of physical interest, including the construction of the Green functions and the functional determinants for the
bc
system, the scalar, and the spinor fields on the torus. In particular, we shall show how the singular terms in the operator product expansion of holomorphic fields for the
bc
system essentially determine arbitrary correlation functions on the torus in terms of elliptic functions.

5.1

Quantum fields

A quantum field is a map from a manifold
\Sigma
to a linear operator acting on a vector space
\mathfrak{F}
, or Fock space, which is equipped with a Hermitian inner product. If the inner product is positive definite then
\mathfrak{F}
is a Hilbert space familar from standard quantum theory, but we shall consider quantum field theories where the inner product is not necessarily definite as well. We shall be interested in the special case where the manifold
\Sigma
is a (connected) Riemann surface which may be compact or non-compact, with or without a boundary. Quantum fields on the complex plane
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}
describe the continuum limit and critical phenomena of two-dimensional statistical mechanics systems, such as the Ising, Potts, XY, and non-linear sigma models. Quantum fields on a general Riemann surface
\Sigma
provide the proper setting for string perturbation theory, where probability amplitudes are obtained via an expansion in powers of the string coupling constant. The expansion is obtained by summing contributions from Riemann surfaces of all genera and, for each genus, by integrating over the corresponding moduli space of Riemann surfaces, as will be explained in more detail in section
12
.

5.1.1

Conformal fields

A conformal field
\phi
on
\Sigma
may be defined as follows. We consider two arbitrary coordinate charts
{\cal U},{\cal U}^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
with local complex coordinate systems
(z,\bar{z})
and
(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})
. Assuming that these coordinate charts have a non-empty intersection
{\cal U}\cap\,{\cal U}^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
, the coordinate systems in the overlap
{\cal U}\cap\,{\cal U}^{\prime}
are related to one another by a holomorphic transition function
z^{\prime}=f(z)
whose derivative
f^{\prime}(z)
is non-zero throughout
{\cal U}\,\cap\,{\cal U}^{\prime}
. Any such transformation of the form
z\to z^{\prime}=f(z)
is a locally conformal transformation, namely it preserves all angles. For more details see appendix
B
. In the coordinate systems
(z,\bar{z})
and
(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})
the field
\phi
is given by
\phi(z,\bar{z})
and
\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})
, respectively. The field
\phi
is a conformal (primary) field of conformal weight
(h,\tilde{h})
, provided
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are related as follows,

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})(dz^{\prime})^{h}(d\bar{z}^{\prime})^{\tilde{h}}=\phi(z,\bar{z})(dz)^{h}(d\bar{z})^{\tilde{h}}

(5.1.1)

or in view of the holomorphicity of
f(z)
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(z)^{h}\,\overline{f^{\prime}(z)}^{\tilde{h}}\,\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})=\phi(z,\bar{z})

(5.1.2)

Equivalently, a conformal field may be viewed as a section of an operator-valued holomorphic line bundle over
\Sigma
. Clearly,
h
and
\tilde{h}
must be the same for all charts
{\cal U}\subset\Sigma
.

Local conformal transformations form an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra, referred to as the Virasoro algebra. Its generators
L_{m}
for
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
obey the following structure relations,

\displaystyle{}[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n}+{\mathfrak{c}\over 12}m(m^{2}-1)\delta_{m+n,0}

(5.1.3)

Here
\mathfrak{c}
is a real-valued constant referred to as the central charge of the Virasoro algebra. The last term is proportional to the identity operator in the representation space of the Virasoro algebra, and therefore commutes with all
L_{m}
. When
\mathfrak{c}\not=0
, the Virasoro algebra is a central extension of the Witt algebra, which has
\mathfrak{c}=0
.

Actually, conformal transformations may be allowed to act independently on the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic coordinates, in which case we have two copies of the Virasoro algebra, whose generators are denoted
L_{n}
acting on
z
and
\tilde{L}_{n}
acting on
\bar{z}
. These two Virasoro algebras commute so that
[L_{m},\tilde{L}_{n}]=0
for all
m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The conformal weights
h,\tilde{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}
characterize the representation of the Virasoro algebra under which the field
\phi
transforms,

\displaystyle{}[L_{m},\phi(z,\bar{z})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z^{m+1}\partial_{z}\phi(z,\bar{z})+h(m+1)z^{m}\phi(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle{}[\tilde{L}_{m},\phi(z,\bar{z})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{z}^{m+1}\partial_{\bar{z}}\phi(z,\bar{z})+\tilde{h}(m+1)\bar{z}^{m}\phi(z,\bar{z})

(5.1.4)

The
minimal models
correspond to the irreducible representations of the Virasoro algebra for
0<\mathfrak{c}<1
, for which the spectrum of allowed conformal weights
h
(of primary conformal fields) is finite. Minimal models have rational conformal weights
h,\tilde{h}
and a rational central charge
\mathfrak{c}
. They are special cases of the more general class of “rational conformal field theories,” discussed further in section
13.6
. For
0<\mathfrak{c}<1
, the unitary minimal models constitute a further subclass of conformal field theories that are so-called unitary, and in particular are associated with
\mathfrak{F}
being a genuine Hilbert space. Many conformal field theories may be realized in terms of free fields.

5.1.2

Free conformal fields

We shall limit our examples in the sequel to free conformal field theories. A free quantum field satisfies a linear partial differential equation on
\Sigma
, given by a differential operator
{\cal D}
. The order of the operator
{\cal D}
is usually of order no larger than 2. All correlation functions of a free quantum field may be evaluated in terms of the Green function
{\cal D}^{-1}
and the functional determinant of
{\cal D}
. For free conformal fields on an arbitrary genus-one Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the correlators and determinants may be expressed in terms of elliptic functions and modular forms. It turns out that the quantization of free conformal fields already provides a sufficiently rich structure for all of string perturbation theory on an arbitrary flat space-time manifold, including Minkowski space-time with or without partial toroidal compactification, as will be further detailed in sections
12
and
13
.

5.2

The
bc
system

An important example of a free conformal field theory on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, that has an immediate connection with elliptic functions and modular forms, is provided by a system of two quantum fields
b
and
c
governed by a first order action
S[b,c]
,

\displaystyle S[b,c]={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d^{2}z\,b(z,\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}c(z,\bar{z})\hskip 72.26999ptd^{2}z={i\over 2}dz\wedge d\bar{z}

(5.2.1)

Invariance of
S[b,c]
under local conformal transformations requires both fields to have
\tilde{h}=0
and their remaining conformal weights to be conjugate: if
b
has conformal weight
(h,0)
then
c
must have conformal weight
(1-h,0)
. The field equations for
b
and
c
are obtained using the variational principle for
S[b,c]
, and are given by,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}b=\partial_{\bar{z}}c=0

(5.2.2)

so that
b
and
c
are locally holomorphic functions of
z
on
\Sigma
. The quantum field theory with these assignments is referred to as the
bc
system of weight
h
. Below we shall consider the
bc
system on the annulus and on the torus. The
bc
system on a Riemann surface of arbitrary genus will be discussed in appendix
D.7
.

5.2.1

The
bc
system on an annulus

An annulus is conformally isomorphic to a cylinder. Choosing the local complex coordinate
z^{\prime}=x+iy
on the cylinder with
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
and the periodic identification
x\approx x+1
, the coordinate
z
on the annulus is given by the conformal map
z=e^{2\pi iz^{\prime}}=e^{2\pi i(x+iy)}
. Holomorphicity and periodicity of the fields
b
and
c
on the cylinder produce the following Fourier expansion in the coordinate
z^{\prime}=x+iy
,
12
12

12

We use proportionality signs here in order to leave the precise normalization to be fixed on the annulus.

\displaystyle b^{\prime}(z^{\prime})\sim\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{n}e^{-2\pi in(x+iy)}\hskip 72.26999ptc^{\prime}(z^{\prime})\sim\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}c_{n}e^{-2\pi in(x+iy)}

(5.2.3)

If the fields
b
and
c
have conformal weights
(h,0)
and
(1-h,0)
respectively, then the fields on the annulus are obtained by the conformal transformation (
5.1.2
), and the Fourier expansions become Laurent expansions in
z=e^{2\pi iz^{\prime}}
given as follows,

\displaystyle b(z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{n}\,z^{-n-h}\hskip 72.26999ptc(z)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}c_{n}z^{-n-1+h}

(5.2.4)

Considering the fields
b
and
c
as quantum operators, the coefficients
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
are operators. The closest connection with holomorphic and meromorphic functions will be obtained by requiring the operators to satisfy anti-commutation relations,

\displaystyle\{b_{m},b_{n}\}=\{c_{m},c_{n}\}=0\hskip 72.26999pt\{b_{m},c_{n}\}=\delta_{m+n,0}

(5.2.5)

where
\{A,B\}=AB+BA
is the anti-commutator and
\delta_{m+n,0}
is the Kronecker symbol. We choose a polarization in which
b_{n}
and
c_{n}
are annihilation operators for
n>0
while
b_{n}^{\dagger}=b_{-n}
and
c_{n}^{\dagger}=c_{-n}
are creation operators. The ground state
|0\rangle
is annihilated by all
b_{n},c_{n}
for
n>0
. The anti-commutation relations for
b_{0},c_{0}
define a Clifford-Dirac algebra whose unique irreducible representation has dimension 2. We shall define the ground state by,

\displaystyle b_{0}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 0|c_{0}=0

\displaystyle b_{n}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle c_{n}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle n\geq 1

(5.2.6)

The Fock space
\mathfrak{F}
is then generated by applying arbitrary polynomials in
b_{n}^{\dagger}
and
c_{n}^{\dagger}
for all possible values of
n>0
to the ground state
|0\rangle
.

5.2.2

The operator product expansion

The fields
b(z)
and
c(z)
are holomorphic in
z
on the annulus. The product
c(z)b(w)
of operators, however, is singular as
z\to w
. This may be seen by applying the operator to the ground state and using the fact that
|0\rangle
is annihilated by all
b_{n}
with
n>0
,

\displaystyle c(z)b(w)|0\rangle=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}c_{m}z^{-m-1+h}\sum_{n\leq 0}b_{n}w^{-n-h}|0\rangle

(5.2.7)

The contributions from
m\leq 0
are non-singular as
z\to w
while for
m>0
we may replace the product
c_{m}b_{n}
by the anti-commutator
\{c_{m},b_{n}\}=\delta_{m+n,0}
in view of
c_{m}|0\rangle=0
. The resulting series is geometric in
w/z
, absolutely convergent for
|z/w|<1
, and may be analytically continued in
z
and
w
throughout the annulus,

\displaystyle c(z)b(w)|0\rangle=\sum_{m>0}z^{-m-1+h}w^{m-h}|0\rangle+\hbox{ regular }={1\over z-w}\,|0\rangle+\hbox{ regular }

(5.2.8)

where “regular” stands for all contributions that have a finite limit as
z\to w
. The pole in
z-w
arises due to the infinite nature of the series and is universal in the following sense. If we apply the operator
c(z)b(w)
to an arbitrary Fock space state, obtained by acting on
|0\rangle
with an arbitrary polynomial in
b_{n}^{\dagger}
and
c_{n}^{\dagger}
with
n>0
, then only a finite number of terms in the infinite series (
5.2.8
) will be affected, which leaves the singularity unchanged. For this reason, one promotes the singularity to an operator statement,

\displaystyle c(z)b(w)={I_{\mathfrak{F}}\over z-w}+\hbox{ regular }

(5.2.9)

where
I_{\mathfrak{F}}
is the identity operator in the Fock space
\mathfrak{F}
. The Laurent series for the operator
b(z)c(w)
is absolutely convergent for
|w/z|<1
, produces the pole
I_{\mathfrak{F}}/(z-w)
, and may be analytically continued to arbitrary
z,w
in the annulus. Comparing with the pole in
c(z)b(w)
, we observe that the sign reversal is consistent with the fact that
b
and
c
satisfy anti-commutation relations. Actually, the pole term is only the first in an infinite Laurent series expansion of the operator
c(z)b(w)
in powers of
z-w
. The general expansion is referred to as the operator product expansion of the operators
c(z)
and
b(w)
,

\displaystyle c(z)b(w)={I_{\mathfrak{F}}\over z-w}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-w)^{n}j^{(n+1)}(w)

(5.2.10)

The coefficients
j^{(n+1)}(w)
for
n>0
are conformal primary fields of weight
(n+1,0)
. The transformation law for
j^{(1)}
is more complicated and will be discussed later. The operators
b(z)b(w)
and
c(z)c(w)
similarly have operator product expansions. In view of the anti-commutation relations (
5.2.5
) we have
b(z)^{2}=c(z)^{2}=0
and,

\displaystyle b(z)b(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(z-w)(\partial_{w}b(w))\,b(w)+{\cal O}\big((z-w)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle c(z)c(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(z-w)(\partial_{w}c(w))\,c(w)+{\cal O}\big((z-w)^{2}\big)

(5.2.11)

so that the operator product expansion is entirely regular.

5.3

Correlators of the
bc
system on the torus

We now consider the
bc
system on an arbitrary torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
. The fields
b
and
c
are meromorphic and doubly periodic, and are thus elliptic functions. On the torus the
bc
systems for different integer values of
h
are all isomorphic to one another, in view of the fact that the torus has a holomorphic differential
dz
which is nowhere vanishing. As a result, a field
\phi(z)(dz)^{h}
of conformal weight
(h,0)
is isomorphic to a scalar field
\phi(z)
of weight
(0,0)
. This isomorphism allows us for a direct evaluation of all correlators of
b,c
fields on the torus. A correlator is the matrix element in the vacuum state
|0\rangle
of a product of
b
and
c
fields evaluated at distinct points on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle A=\langle 0|c(z_{1})\cdots c(z_{N})b(w_{1})\cdots b(w_{M})|0\rangle

(5.3.1)

The correlators are meromorphic in
z_{n}
for all
n=1,\cdots,N
and in
w_{m}
for all
m=1,\cdots,M
.
We shall now show that the positions of their zeros and poles in each variable are known from the operator product expansions discussed earlier. In view of (
5.2.2
), the correlator has a simple zero when two points
z_{n}
and
z_{n^{\prime}}
coincide, as well as when two points
w_{m}
and
w_{m^{\prime}}
coincide. In view of (
5.2.9
), the correlator has a simple pole whenever a point
z_{n}
approaches a point
w_{m}
, whose residue is given by a correlator with one fewer
b
and one fewer
c
,

\displaystyle A={(-)^{N-1+m-n}\over z_{n}-w_{m}}\langle 0|c(z_{1})\cdots\widehat{c(z_{n})}\cdots c(z_{N})b(w_{1})\cdots\widehat{b(w_{m})}\cdots b(w_{M})|0\rangle+\hbox{ regular }\quad

(5.3.2)

The sign factor arises from permuting the positions of the fields, the hatted operators are to be omitted, and “regular” includes all terms that are non-singular as
z_{n}\to w_{m}
.

Considered as a function of
z_{n}
, the correlator has precisely
M
simple poles at
w_{1},\cdots,w_{M}
. In view of Theorem
2.3
it must have
M
zeros in
z_{n}
. These zeros must include the
N-1
points
z_{n^{\prime}}
with
n^{\prime}\not=n
, so we must have
M\geq N-1
. Similarly, as a function of
w_{m}
, the correlator has precisely
N
simple poles at
z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}
. In view of Theorem
2.3
it must have
N
zeros in
w_{m}
which must include the
M-1
points
w_{m^{\prime}}
with
m^{\prime}\not=m
, so we must also have
N\geq M-1
. Combing both inequalities allows for either
M=N
or
M-N=\pm 1
. The latter is excluded by symmetry under reversing the sign of both
b
and
c
. Thus only
M=N
gives a non-vanishing correlator. We shall now evaluate the correlators
A
for
M=N
in terms of
\vartheta
-functions using Theorems
2.3
,
2.6
, and
2.7
.

5.3.1

The 4-point correlator

We begin with the correlator
\langle 0|c(z_{1})c(z_{2})b(w_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle
. As a function of
z_{1}
, it has a simple zero at
z_{2}
, simple poles at
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, and no other poles. In view of the second relation in Theorem
2.3
, it must have a second zero, whose position is determined by the third relation in Theorem
2.3
to be given by
-z_{2}+w_{1}+w_{2}
(mod
\Lambda
). Proceeding analogously for
z_{2}
using Theorem
2.6
we obtain the following expression for the correlator,

\displaystyle\langle 0|c(z_{1})c(z_{2})b(w_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle=A_{0}\,{\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}+z_{2}-w_{1}-w_{2})\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\vartheta_{1}(w_{1}-w_{2})\over\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-w_{1})\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-w_{2})\vartheta_{1}(z_{2}-w_{1})\vartheta_{1}(z_{2}-w_{2})}

(5.3.3)

Here,
A_{0}
is independent of
z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}
and all dependence on
\tau
has been suppressed. We may use this expression to obtain the correlator
\langle 0|c(z_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle
by letting
z_{2}\to w_{1}
,

\displaystyle\langle 0|c(z_{1})c(z_{2})b(w_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle={1\over z_{2}-w_{1}}\,{A_{0}\over\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0)}+\hbox{ regular}

(5.3.4)

The two-point correlator is constant and saturated by the zero modes of the
c
and
b
fields,

\displaystyle\langle 0|c(z_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle={A_{0}\over\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0)}

(5.3.5)

consistently with the fact that no function on a torus can have a single simple pole. Alternatively, we may use Theorem
2.7
to express the correlator in terms of the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function and its derivatives. Since the poles in
z_{1},z_{2}
of the correlator at
w_{1},w_{2}
are simple, and must have opposite residue, we have,

\displaystyle\langle 0|c(z_{1})c(z_{2})b(w_{1})b(w_{2})|0\rangle=A_{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}A_{i}\big\{\zeta(z_{i}-w_{1})-\zeta(z_{i}-w_{2})\big\}

(5.3.6)

where
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
are independent of
z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}
. Matching the residues with (
5.3.4
), we find
A_{1}=-A_{2}=-A_{0}/\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
. This condition also shows that the correlator has zeros at
z_{1}=z_{2}
and
w_{1}=w_{2}
provided
A_{3}=0
. We leave it as an exercise to the reader to express the correlator in terms of the Weierstrass
\wp
function and to show that these three expressions may be related to one another with explicit identities.

5.3.2

Arbitrary correlators

Correlators for arbitrary
N
may be evaluated iteratively. As a function of
z_{1}
the correlator has
N
simple poles at
w_{1},\cdots,w_{N}
, and
N-1
known zeros at
z_{2},\cdots,z_{N}
. The missing zero is given by Theorem
2.4
by the sum
-\sum_{n=2}^{N}z_{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{n}
. Putting all together, we have,

\displaystyle A=\hat{A}_{1}\,{\vartheta_{1}(D)\,\prod_{n=2}^{N}\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-z_{n})\over\prod_{n=1}^{N}\vartheta_{1}(z_{1}-w_{n})}\hskip 72.26999ptD=\sum_{n=1}^{N}(z_{n}-w_{n})

(5.3.7)

where
\hat{A}_{1}
is independent of
z_{1}
, but depends on all other
z_{n}
and
w_{n}
. Again, all dependence on
\tau
has been suppressed. Proceeding iteratively on the points
z_{n}
we find that the factor
\vartheta_{1}(D)
always accounts for the missing zero. Putting all together, we obtain,

\displaystyle A=A_{0}\,{\vartheta_{1}(D)\,\prod_{m<n}\vartheta_{1}(z_{m}-z_{n})\vartheta_{1}(w_{m}-w_{n})\over\prod_{m,n}\vartheta_{1}(z_{m}-w_{n})}

(5.3.8)

where
A_{0}
depends only on
\tau
. We leave it as an exercise for the reader to express the correlator also in terms of Weierstrass
\wp
- and
\zeta
-functions using Theorems
2.4
and
2.7
respectively.

5.4

The scalar field

A real-valued scalar field
\varphi
is governed by a second order differential operator on the torus, namely the Laplace-Beltrami operator. In local complex coordinates
(z,\bar{z})
on the torus
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
, its action may be expressed as follows,

\displaystyle S[\varphi]={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d^{2}z\,\partial_{z}\varphi\,\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi

(5.4.1)

The corresponding field equation
\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi=0
implies that
\partial_{z}\varphi
is a locally holomorphic field while
\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi
is a locally anti-holomorphic field. On an annulus, these quantum fields admit Laurent expansions,

\displaystyle i\partial_{z}\varphi(z,\bar{z})=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{m}\,z^{-m-1}\hskip 72.26999pti\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi(z,\bar{z})=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\tilde{\varphi}_{m}\,\bar{z}^{-m-1}

(5.4.2)

where the coefficient operators
\varphi_{m}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}
satisfy commutation relations,

\displaystyle{}[\varphi_{m},\varphi_{n}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}\hskip 72.26999pt{}[\tilde{\varphi}_{m},\tilde{\varphi}_{n}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}

(5.4.3)

We choose a polarization such that
\varphi_{-n}=\varphi_{n}^{\dagger}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{-n}=\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\dagger}
, and define the ground state
|0\rangle
to be annihilated by
\varphi_{n}|0\rangle=\tilde{\varphi}_{n}|0\rangle=0
for
n\geq 0
. The Fock space is constructed by applying arbitrary polynomials in
\varphi_{n}^{\dagger}
for
n>0
to
|0\rangle
. Each such state has positive norm so that the Fock space for a scalar field is actually a Hilbert space, which we denote by
\mathfrak{F}
.

The scalar field theory is conformal and has two commuting Virasoro algebras generated by
L_{m}
and
\tilde{L}_{m}
whose expressions in terms of
\varphi_{m}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}
are given as follows,
13
13

13

The offset
-\tfrac{1}{24}
in
L_{0}
arises from the conformal transformation of the expression on the cylinder (which has no such offset) to the annulus. It may also be viewed as the sum of the zero-point energies of the harmonic oscillators, using the fact that the sum of all positive integers
m
gives
\zeta(-1)=-\tfrac{1}{12}
.

\displaystyle L_{0}=-{1\over 24}+{1\over 2}\varphi_{0}^{2}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{-n}\varphi_{n}\hskip 72.26999ptL_{m}={1\over 2}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{m-n}\varphi_{n}\quad\hbox{ for }m\not=0

(5.4.4)

and similarly for
\tilde{L}_{m}
. The operators
\partial_{z}\varphi
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi
are conformal primary fields of weights
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
respectively, and satisfy the corresponding transformations given in (
5.1.1
).

The operator product expansion of two holomorphic fields is given by,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\varphi(z)\partial_{w}\varphi(w)={1\over(z-w)^{2}}-2\,T(w)+{\cal O}(z-w)

(5.4.5)

where the Laurent expansion of the stress tensor
T(w)
for the holomorphic component of the scalar field is given in terms of the Virasoro generators
L_{m}
,

\displaystyle T(w)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}L_{n}\,w^{-n-2}

(5.4.6)

and similarly for the anti-holomorphic field
\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi
and the generators
\tilde{L}_{m}
.

We may attempt to use the operator product expansion (
5.4.5
) to evaluate the correlator of holomorphic fields
\partial_{z}\varphi(z)
, just as we had in the case of the
bc
system. For simplicity, we choose matrix elements between the ground state
|0\rangle=|0,0\rangle
on the torus
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\langle 0|\partial_{z}\varphi(z_{1})\cdots\partial_{z}\varphi(z_{N})|0\rangle

(5.4.7)

The correlator vanishes for odd
N
and is meromorphic in all its points
z_{n}
for even
N
. As a function of
z_{n}
, the correlator has a double pole at every point
z_{m}
with
m\not=n
. For example, for the 2-point correlator, the double pole shows that the correlator must be of the form,

\displaystyle\langle 0|\partial_{z}\varphi(z_{1})\partial_{z}\varphi(z_{2})|0\rangle=\wp(z_{1}-z_{2})+A_{4}

(5.4.8)

where
A_{4}
is independent of
z_{1},z_{2}
. In contrast with the
bc
system, the zeros of the correlator are not manifest now, and the constant
A_{4}
cannot be fixed by using known zeros. It may be determined, however, by using not just the pole term in the operator product expansion, but also the term proportional to the stress tensor
T
, and we find
A_{4}=0
. For higher point correlators, however, the corresponding constant terms will not be so easy to evaluate. Instead we shall turn to evaluating the full correlators of the field
\varphi
itself.

5.5

The scalar Green function on the torus

The scalar Laplace-Beltrami operator on the torus has a zero mode and is not invertible on all scalar functions.
However, an inverse may be defined on its range, namely on functions orthogonal to constant functions on
\Sigma
. To construct this inverse, we shall present two different approaches, the first based on Fourier series, and the second using
\vartheta
-functions.

5.5.1

The Laplacian on the torus

An arbitrary Riemannian metric on the torus
\Sigma
may be rescaled to a flat metric using a
\Sigma
-dependent Weyl transformation, as shown in more detail in appendix
B
. A further rescaling by a constant Weyl transformation allows us to obtain a flat metric with unit area. This reduced metric depends on a single complex modulus parameter
\tau\in{\cal H}
, and we represent the torus by the quotient
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
for the lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{Z}}\tau
. The points on
\Sigma
may be parametrized by a complex coordinate
z=x+y\tau
for
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, for example in the range
0\leq x,y\leq 1
(see Figure
2
). In these coordinates, the flat metric
\mathfrak{g}
and associated volume form
d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
of unit area are given by,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}={|dz|^{2}\over\tau_{2}}={|dx+\tau dy|^{2}\over\tau_{2}}\hskip 72.26999ptd\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}={i\over 2}{dz\wedge d\bar{z}\over\tau_{2}}=dx\wedge dy

(5.5.1)

We define an inner product on the space of complex-valued functions
f_{1},f_{2}:\Sigma\mapsto{\mathbb{C}}
by,

\displaystyle\langle f_{1}|f_{2}\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\,\bar{f}_{1}\,f_{2}

(5.5.2)

making the space of bounded functions on
\Sigma
into an
L^{2}(\Sigma)
Hilbert space. The Cauchy-Riemann operators in these coordinates take the form,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}={1\over 2i\tau_{2}}(\partial_{y}-\bar{\tau}\partial_{x})\hskip 72.26999pt\partial_{\bar{z}}=-{1\over 2i\tau_{2}}(\partial_{y}-\tau\partial_{x})

(5.5.3)

while the Laplace-Beltrami operator, or simply Laplacian, is given by,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}=-4\tau_{2}\partial_{\bar{z}}\partial_{z}=-{1\over\tau_{2}}(\partial_{y}-\tau\partial_{x})(\partial_{y}-\bar{\tau}\partial_{x})

(5.5.4)

The Laplacian is self-adjoint with respect to the inner product (
5.5.2
) on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
and positive. It is invariant under translations in
x,y
and thus commutes with the operators
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
that generate infinitesimal translations in the
x
and
y
directions respectively. For fixed
\tau
, the Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
has a discrete spectrum with a single zero mode, namely the constant function on
\Sigma
, so that
{\rm Ker\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}}={\mathbb{C}}
. Its range is the set of functions orthogonal to constant functions.

5.5.2

The scalar Green function via Fourier series

Since the self-adjoint operators
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
mutually commute and commute with
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
, the Laplacian is diagonal in a basis where
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
are simultaneously diagonal. Such a basis is given by the following orthonormal basis of doubly periodic functions on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle f_{m,n}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i(nx-my)}

\displaystyle\langle f_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}|f_{m,n}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{m^{\prime},m}\,\delta_{n^{\prime},n}\hskip 72.26999ptm,m^{\prime},n,n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(5.5.5)

They also satisfy the completeness relation,

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\overline{f_{m,n}}(u,v)f_{m,n}(x,y)=\delta(x-u)\delta(y-v)

(5.5.6)

where the Dirac
\delta
functions on the right are periodic in their argument with period 1.
One verifies that the Laplace operator is indeed diagonal in this basis, and we find,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\,f_{m,n}=\lambda_{m,n}(\tau)\,f_{m,n}\hskip 72.26999pt\lambda_{m,n}(\tau)=4\pi^{2}\,{|m+n\tau|^{2}\over\tau_{2}}

(5.5.7)

The zero eigenvalue corresponds to the constant function with
m=n=0
, while all other eigenvalues are strictly positive, as expected. The Green function is customarily defined as the inverse of
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}/4\pi
on the space of functions orthogonal to constants. In view of translation invariance, we have
G(z,w|\tau)=G(z-w|\tau)
with
z=x+y\tau
, and
G(z|\tau)
is given by,

\displaystyle G(z|\tau)=\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}{\tau_{2}\over\pi|m+n\tau|^{2}}\,e^{2\pi i(nx-my)}

(5.5.8)

Applying the Laplacian, and using the completeness relation (
5.5.2
), one verifies,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}G(z-w|\tau)=4\pi\tau_{2}\,\delta^{(2)}(z-w)-4\pi

(5.5.9)

where the 2-dimensional
\delta
-function, with
w=u+\tau v
, is defined by,

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\,\delta^{(2)}(z-w)=\delta(x-u)\delta(y-v)

(5.5.10)

By construction, the right side of (
5.5.9
) is orthogonal to constant functions. Actually, the Green function obtained in (
5.5.8
) itself is orthogonal to constant functions,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}(z)\,G(z-w|\tau)=0

(5.5.11)

since the zero mode
(m,n)=(0,0)
has been removed from the sum in (
5.5.8
).

5.5.3

The scalar Green function via
\vartheta
-functions

We may take equations (
5.5.9
) and (
5.5.11
) as defining
G
and express both in terms of the complex variables
z,w
,

\displaystyle-\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}G(z-w|\tau)=\pi\delta^{(2)}(z-w)-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}

(5.5.12)

The solution for
G
is easy to construct in terms of
\vartheta
-functions, as we shall now show. This method will generalize to constructing the Green function on Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus, whereas the method of Fourier series of the preceding section does not generalize since higher genus Riemann surfaces have no continuous isometries.

We proceed as follows for fixed
\tau\in{\cal H}
. For
|z-w|\ll 1
and
|z-w|\ll\tau_{2}
, the global structure of the torus is negligible and the Green function must approach its expression on
{\mathbb{C}}
which is given by
G(z-w|\tau)\approx-\ln|z-w|^{2}
. The function
\vartheta_{1}(z-w|\tau)/\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)
behaves as
z-w
for small
|z-w|
and is therefore a reasonable candidate to play the role of
z-w
on the torus. However,
\vartheta_{1}(z-w|\tau)
cannot be a doubly periodic function as it is holomorphic and would then have to be constant by Liouville’s theorem. Still, given the transformation laws of
\vartheta_{1}(z-w|\tau)
under
z\to z+1
and
z\to z+\tau
given in (
2.6.2
), one readily finds a simple additive term to restore double periodicity, and
G(z,w|\tau)=G(z-w|\tau)
is given by,

\displaystyle G(z|\tau)=-\ln\left|{\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)\over\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)}\right|^{2}+2\pi{({\rm Im\,}z)^{2}\over\tau_{2}}+G_{0}(\tau)

(5.5.13)

Upon applying the Laplace operator, the first term produces the
\delta
-function, while the second term produces the constant on the right side of (
5.5.12
). The
z
-independent term
G_{0}(\tau)
may be determined by imposing the normalization condition (
5.5.11
).

To compute
G_{0}(\tau)
it is convenient to choose the range of integration
-{1\over 2}\leq x,y\leq{1\over 2}
. The integrals of the last two terms in
G
are readily evaluated. To compute the integral of the first term in
G
we use the product formula for
\vartheta_{1}
given in (
2.7
), expand the logarithm of the factors
(1-q^{n}e^{\pm 2\pi iz})
in powers of
q
, observe that their integral vanishes term-by-term, and finally compute the integral of
\ln|\sin(\pi z)|^{2}
by the same expansion method. Putting all together, we obtain the properly normalized scalar Green function,

\displaystyle G(z|\tau)=-\ln\left|{\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}+2\pi{({\rm Im\,}z)^{2}\over\tau_{2}}

(5.5.14)

The expression (
5.5.8
) for
G
may be related to (
5.5.14
) by carrying out the sum over
m
first in (
5.5.8
) using the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.1

The Lipschitz formula for
{\rm Re\,}(z)>0
,
{\rm Re\,}(s)>0
and
0<\alpha\leq 1
is given by,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{e^{2\pi in\alpha}\over(z+in)^{s}}={(2\pi)^{s}\over\Gamma(s)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(m+\alpha)^{s-1}\,e^{-2\pi z(m+\alpha)}

(5.5.15)

The proof may be found in Rademacher
[
46
]
.

5.5.4

Modular properties

A modular transformation
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
leaves the metric
\mathfrak{g}
and the Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
invariant provided
\tau
and the complex coordinate
z
transform according to the following rules,

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}=\gamma\tau={a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\hskip 50.58878ptz^{\prime}=\gamma z={z\over c\tau+d}\hskip 50.58878pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)

(5.5.16)

The transformations on the real coordinates are as follows,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}x^{\prime}\cr y^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}a&-b\cr-c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}x\cr y\end{matrix}\right)

(5.5.17)

The spectrum of the Laplacian is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, as the eigenvalues
\lambda_{m,n}(\tau)
of
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
transform as follows,

\displaystyle\lambda_{m,n}(x,y)=\lambda_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}m^{\prime}\cr n^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}a&-b\cr-c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}m\cr n\end{matrix}\right)

(5.5.18)

in view of the relation
n^{\prime}x^{\prime}-m^{\prime}y^{\prime}=nx-my
. It readily follows that also the normalized Green function, given in (
5.5.8
), is modular invariant,

\displaystyle G\left({z-w\over c\tau+d}\bigg|{a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\right)=G(z-w|\tau)

(5.5.19)

Modular invariance may be verified directly on the expression for the Green function in terms of
\vartheta
-functions in (
5.5.14
) by using the transformation laws of
\vartheta_{1}
given in (
3.8
).

5.6

Scalar determinant and the first Kronecker limit formula

The functional determinant of
\Delta=\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}/4\pi
, considered as an operator on the subspace of functions orthogonal to constants, is formally given by the product of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{m,n}(\tau)/4\pi
. However, this product does not converge. To define the determinant we introduce the following
\zeta
-function associated with
\Delta
,

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Delta}(\tau,s)=\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}\left({4\pi\over\lambda_{m,n}}\right)^{s}=E_{s}(\tau)

(5.6.1)

where
E_{s}(\tau)
is the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series introduced in section
4.2
. The series for
E_{s}(\tau)
is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
. Its analytic continuation to
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
has a simple pole at
s=1
, is modular invariant, and satisfies the functional relation,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)=\Gamma(1-s)E_{1-s}(\tau)

(5.6.2)

Taking the limit
s\to 0
of (
4.2.16
) gives
\zeta_{\Delta}(\tau,0)=-1
, while the determinant is given by,

\displaystyle\ln{\rm Det}^{\prime}\Delta=-\zeta_{\Delta}^{\prime}(\tau,0)=-\partial_{s}E_{s}(\tau)\Big|_{s=0}

(5.6.3)

To compute the determinant, we use the expressions for
E_{s}(\tau)
in terms of the functions
A_{s}(\tau)
and
B_{s}(\tau)
defined in (
4.2.2
) and (
4.2.13
),

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(\tau,0)=A_{0}(\tau)+{\pi\tau_{2}\over 3}-\ln\tau_{2}-\ln(4\pi)

(5.6.4)

The last three terms on the right side arise from differentiating
B_{s}(\tau)/\Gamma(s)
at
s=0
. To compute
A_{0}(\tau)
, we simplify the summand in (
4.2.13
) by using the expression for the modified Bessel function in terms of elementary functions,
K_{-{1\over 2}}(x)=\sqrt{\pi/2x}\,e^{-x}
. We discover that
A_{0}(\tau)
becomes a sum over holomorphic and anti-holomorphic dependence on
\tau
,

\displaystyle A_{0}(\tau)=\sum_{m,n\not=0}{1\over|m|}\,\exp\Big\{2\pi imn\tau_{1}-2\pi|mn|\tau_{2}\Big\}

(5.6.5)

which may be compactly expressed as follows,

\displaystyle A_{0}(\tau)=-2\ln\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{m})(1-\bar{q}^{m})

(5.6.6)

We may express the final combined result in terms of the Dedekind
\eta(\tau)
function,

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(\tau,0)=-\ln\left(4\pi\tau_{2}|\eta(\tau)|^{4}\right)

(5.6.7)

Combining this with
\zeta_{\Delta}(\tau,0)=-1
and (
5.6.3
), we obtain,

\displaystyle{\rm Det}^{\prime}\Delta=4\pi\tau_{2}|\eta(\tau)|^{4}=4\pi{\rm Det}^{\prime}\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}

(5.6.8)

which is manifestly modular invariant.

5.6.1

The first Kronecker limit formula

The first Kronecker limit formula gives the residue and the finite part of
\zeta_{\Delta}(\tau,s)
at its pole
s=1
by the following expression,

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Delta}(\tau,s)={a_{-1}\over s-1}+a_{0}+{\cal O}(s-1)

(5.6.9)

Using the functional relation (
5.6.2
), it is immediate that
a_{-1}=-1
. From the same formula we get also
a_{0}
by,

\displaystyle a_{0}={d\over ds}\Big((s-1)E_{s}(\tau)\Big)\Big|_{s=1}=-\ln\left(4\pi\tau_{2}|\eta(\tau)|^{4}\right)+2\gamma

(5.6.10)

where
\gamma
is Euler’s constant as it arises in
\Gamma^{\prime}(1)=-\gamma
.

5.6.2

The scalar partition function

The scalar determinant is intimately related to the partition function for the free scalar field in statistical mechanics. A partition function
Z(\beta)
for a Hamiltonian
H
acting in a Hilbert space
\mathfrak{F}
, evaluated at an inverse temperature
\beta
is given by,

\displaystyle Z(\beta)={\rm Tr}_{\mathfrak{F}}\left(e^{-\beta H}\right)

(5.6.11)

where
{\rm Tr}_{\mathfrak{F}}
is the trace evaluated in the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{F}
. The scalar field has two independent sets of oscillators, namely
\varphi_{m}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{m}
, with independent Virasoro generators. In particular, the generators
L_{0}
and
\tilde{L}_{0}
are given by a sum of decoupled harmonic oscillator Hamiltonians plus the term due to the zero mode.
Instead of the real-valued inverse temperature
\beta>0
, we shall complexify
\beta
and let
\beta\to-2\pi i\tau
whose real part is still positive in view of
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
. The partition function for
L_{0}
is then given as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left(q^{L_{0}}\right)=q^{-\tfrac{1}{24}+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over(1-q^{n})}=q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}}\,\eta(\tau)^{-1}

(5.6.12)

where the trace is taken only over the oscillator part of the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{F}
. Proceeding similarly for
\tilde{L}_{0}
and complexifying its inverse temperature
\beta\to 2\pi i\bar{\tau}
, we obtain the combined partition function,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\tilde{L}_{0}}\right)=q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}}\bar{q}^{\tfrac{1}{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{0}^{2}}{1\over|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}

(5.6.13)

The zero mode operators
\varphi_{0}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{0}
are self-adjoint and have a continuous spectrum of real eigenvalues. Physically, these eigenvalues are the momenta of left- and right-movers. Periodicity in
\tau_{1}\to\tau_{1}+1
requires
\varphi_{0}^{2}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0}^{2}\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
. When the field
\varphi
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}
, we require the eigenvalues of
\varphi_{0}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{0}
to be equal to one another, and to range over
{\mathbb{R}}
. The full trace is then given by,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(q^{L_{0}}\bar{q}^{\tilde{L}_{0}}\right)={1\over|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dp\,|q|^{p^{2}}={1\over\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}=\left({\rm Det}^{\prime}\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\right)^{-{1\over 2}}

(5.6.14)

which is precisely the modular invariant partition function evaluated using the functional integral.

5.7

Spinor fields

The orthogonal Lie algebra
SO(d)
has spinor representations in addition to its tensorial representations. A spinor representation may be viewed either as a double-valued representation of the group
SO(d)
or as a single-valued representation of the double cover
Spin(d)
. A manifold supports spinor fields provided it is orientable and its second Stiefel-Whitney class vanishes. These conditions are satisfied in
d=2
for Riemann surfaces. In terms of bundles, one decomposes the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\Sigma
into a direct sum of its holomorphic and anti-holomorphic part
T^{*}\Sigma=K\oplus K^{*}
where
K
is referred to as the canonical bundle. A spin bundle
S
is a holomorphic line bundle whose square is isomorphic to the canonical bundle
S^{\otimes 2}\approx K
, and a spinor field is then an operator-valued section of
S
. For more details on Riemann surfaces see appendix
B
, and for the general construction of line bundles on an arbitrary Riemann surface see appendix
C
.

Different realizations of spinor fields, corresponding to different spin bundles, on a given Riemann surface
\Sigma
, are referred to as spin structures. They are labeled by the first homology class of the surface with
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
coefficients, namely
H_{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. For example, the homology group of a compact genus
g
Riemann surface is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2g}
and therefore the different spin structures are labeled by
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
on each one of the
2g
independent homology cycle.

For the annulus, there is a single homology cycle, and thus two different spin structures, referred to as Neveu-Schwarz (NS) or Ramond (R). Denoting a holomorphic spinor field of conformal weight
(h,0)
by
\psi
, its Laurent series on the cylinder and on the annulus, with coordinates
z^{\prime}=x+iy
and
z=e^{2\pi iz^{\prime}}
respectively, are given as follows,

NS

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}+{1\over 2}}\psi_{r}\,e^{-2\pi ir(x+iy)}

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}+{1\over 2}}\psi_{r}\,z^{-r-h}

R

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{n}\,e^{-2\pi in(x+iy)}

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{n}\,z^{-r-h}

(5.7.1)

A free spinor field on a Riemann surface is governed by a first order differential operator, namely by a generalized
bc
system in which the fields
b
and
c
are allowed either NS or R identifications. An important special case is that of a spinor field of conformal weight
({1\over 2},0)
, in which case the fields
b
and
c
have the same conformal weight
({1\over 2},0)
and are quantized using anti-commutation relations as in the
bc
system we discussed earlier.
14
14

14

Another important case is the superghost
\beta\gamma
system where
b=\beta
has weight
({3\over 2},0)
and
c=\gamma
has weight
(-{1\over 2},0)
and the oscillators obey commutation relations.
In this case the NS spin structure makes the field
\psi(z)
single-valued on the annulus, while the R spin structure makes the field
\psi(z)
double-valued on the annulus.

For the torus, a canonical choice of homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
is given by the identifications
z\to z+1
and
z\to z+\tau
respectively. The four spin structures are distinguished according to whether the field is periodic or anti-periodic around each cycle. The signs are inherited from the cylinder,

\displaystyle\psi(z+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2\pi i\alpha}\psi(z)

\displaystyle\psi(z+\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2\pi i\beta}\psi(z)

(5.7.2)

for
\alpha,\beta\in\{0,\tfrac{1}{2}\}
(mod 1) with the values 0 and
\tfrac{1}{2}
corresponding to NS and R respectively. Under the modular transformations
S
and
T
the four spin structures transform as follows,

\displaystyle S:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{matrix}\right]\rightarrow\left[\begin{matrix}\beta\\
\alpha\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle T:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{matrix}\right]\rightarrow\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha\\
\alpha+\beta+\tfrac{1}{2}\end{matrix}\right]

(5.7.3)

where the addition
\alpha+\beta+\tfrac{1}{2}
is evaluated mod 1. The spin structure
\alpha=\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}
is referred to as the odd spin structure, while the three other spin structures are referred to as even spin structures. Under modular transformations, the odd spin structure maps to itself, while the three even spin structures are permuted amongst one another. This transformation rule precisely corresponds to the transformation rule for Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions with half-integer characteristics given in (
3.8
). This is no accident, as will be further clarified below. Furthermore, in view of the existence of the holomorphic and nowhere vanishing differential
dz
, spinor fields on the torus for a given spin structure but different conformal weights
(h,0)
are all isomorphic to one another. Therefore, we may focus attention on the case
h={1\over 2}
. For two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
with the same spin structure (or more accurately for two sections of a given spin bundle
S
), we define a Hermitian inner product,

\displaystyle\langle f_{1}|f_{2}\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\bar{f}_{1}f_{2}

(5.7.4)

which has a positive definite norm and produces a Hilbert space
L^{2}(\Sigma,S)
.

5.8

Spinor Green functions on the torus

In this subsection, we shall derive the Green function for the spinor field on a torus
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
for each one of the four spin structures. Just as we did for the scalar field, we offer two perspectives: a calculation via Fourier analysis and a calculation directly in terms of
\vartheta
-functions.

5.8.1

Odd spin structure Green function

For the odd spin structure
\alpha=\beta={1\over 2}
, the Green function may be obtained from the results of the scalar case, because the spinor field is doubly periodic and has the constant spinor as a zero mode. We define the Green function
S_{1}(z|\tau)
that satisfies the following equation,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}S_{1}(z-w|\tau)=\pi\delta^{(2)}(z-w)-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}

(5.8.1)

by analogy with the Laplace equation satisfied by the scalar Green function
G(z-w|\tau)
in (
5.5.12
).
The solution is given in terms of the scalar Green function by,

\displaystyle S_{1}(z-w|\tau)=-\partial_{z}G(z-w|\tau)=\partial_{z}\ln\vartheta_{1}(z-w|\tau)+{2\pi i\over\tau_{2}}\,{\rm Im\,}(z-w)

(5.8.2)

up to an additive constant which amounts to adding a term proportional to the Dirac zero mode.
Since
S_{1}(z-w|\tau)
has a single pole in
z
at
w
, and is doubly periodic, it cannot possibly be meromorphic
as no elliptic functions with a single simple pole exist. The second term on the right is present to compensate.
Alternatively, a term-by-term differentiation of the Kronecker-Eisenstein series of
G(z-w|\tau)
in (
5.5.8
) gives the following expression for
S_{1}
,

\displaystyle S_{1}(z-w|\tau)=\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}{e^{2\pi i(nx-my)}\over m+n\tau}

(5.8.3)

The sum is only conditionally convergent and requires regularization. An alternative Green function
\tilde{S}_{1}(z;w_{1},w_{2}|\tau)
allows for two poles in
z
at
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
with opposite residue. Requiring anti-symmetry in
w_{1},w_{2}
produces a unique Green function,

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{1}(z;w_{1},w_{2}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{1}(z-w_{1}|\tau)+S_{1}(w_{1}-w_{2}|\tau)+S_{1}(w_{2}-z|\tau)

(5.8.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta(z-w_{1}|\tau)+\zeta(w_{1}-w_{2}|\tau)+\zeta(w_{2}-z|\tau)

where the expression is recast in terms of the Weierstrass
\zeta
-function on the second line above.

5.8.2

Even spin structure Green functions via Fourier analysis

The chiral Dirac operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
is not self-adjoint. However, it is a linear combination with constant coefficients of two self-adjoint operators
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}={1\over 2\tau_{2}}(i\partial_{y}-\tau\,i\partial_{x})

(5.8.5)

Since the operators
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
mutually commute, the operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
may be diagonalized in the basis in which
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
are diagonal. However, since the coefficients of the linear combination in (
5.8.5
) are complex, the eigenvalues of
\partial_{\bar{z}}
are complex.

For a given even spin structure
[\kappa]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, the operators
i\partial_{x}
and
i\partial_{y}
are diagonalized in the following basis,

\displaystyle f[\kappa]_{m,n}(x,y)=\exp\Big\{2\pi i((n+\beta+\tfrac{1}{2})x-(m+\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2})y)\Big\}

(5.8.6)

The eigenvalues of the operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
are given by,

\displaystyle\lambda[\kappa]_{m,n}={\pi\over\tau_{2}}\Big(m+\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}+(n+\beta+\tfrac{1}{2})\tau\Big)

(5.8.7)

Since
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)>0
and
\alpha,\beta
do not both equal
\tfrac{1}{2}
mod 1, none of the eigenvalues vanishes. The Green function for the operator
\tau_{2}\partial_{\bar{z}}
is then given by,

\displaystyle S[\kappa](z|\tau)={1\over\tau_{2}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{f[\kappa]_{m,n}(x,y)\over\lambda[\kappa]_{m,n}}=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{e^{2\pi i\{(n+\beta+{1\over 2})x-(m+\alpha+{1\over 2})y\}}\over m+\alpha+{1\over 2}+(n+\beta+{1\over 2})\tau}

(5.8.8)

so that
S[\kappa]
satisfies,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}S[\kappa](z-w|\tau)=\pi\delta(z-w)

(5.8.9)

where the integral of
\delta(z-w)
over
\Sigma
is normalized to
\tau_{2}
.

5.8.3

Even spin structure Green functions via
\vartheta
-functions

We seek a meromorphic function
S[\kappa](z-w|\tau)
, which has a single simple pole in a fundamental parallelogram for
\Lambda
, is doubly periodic with periods in
2\Lambda
, and behaves as follows under translations in
\Lambda/(2\Lambda)
,

\displaystyle S[\kappa](z+1|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2\pi i\alpha}\,S[\kappa](z|\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt[\kappa]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha\cr\beta\cr\end{smallmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle S[\kappa](z+\tau|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2\pi i\beta}\,S[\kappa](z|\tau)

(5.8.10)

For even spin structures
\kappa
, the simple pole in
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
and the above translations are realized uniquely by the ratio
\vartheta[\kappa](z|\tau)/\vartheta_{1}(z|\tau)
, and the normalization of the residue at
z=w
gives,

\displaystyle S[\kappa](z-w|\tau)={\vartheta[\kappa](z-w|\tau)\,\vartheta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)\over\vartheta[\kappa](0|\tau)\,\vartheta_{1}(z-w|\tau)}

(5.8.11)

As an elliptic function in
{\mathbb{C}}/(2\Lambda)
, the Szegö kernel
S[\kappa](z-w|\tau)
has four simple poles, namely at
z\equiv 0,\tau+1
with residue 1, and at
z\equiv 1,\tau
with residue
-1
.

5.9

Spinor determinant and the second Kronecker limit formula

The eigenvalues of the chiral Dirac operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
are complex, rendering the
\zeta
-function approach to evaluating its determinant inapplicable. Instead we shall make use of the fact that the dependence of the operator
\tau_{2}\partial_{\bar{z}}
on the modulus
\tau
is holomorphic and we shall seek to construct an object
{\rm Det}(\partial_{\bar{z}})
that is holomorphic in
\tau
.

What we do know how to evaluate using
\zeta
-function methods is the determinant of the Laplace operator
\Delta_{\kappa}=-4\tau_{2}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}
on a spinor with even spin structure
\kappa
. The Laplace operator is self-adjoint, has positive spectrum, and no zero modes,

\displaystyle\lambda[\kappa]_{m,n}={4\pi^{2}\over\tau_{2}}\big|m+\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau\big|^{2}\hskip 72.26999pt\kappa=\left[\begin{matrix}{1\over 2}+\alpha\\
{1\over 2}+\beta\end{matrix}\right]

(5.9.1)

In fact we may generalize the problem by taking the characteristics
\alpha,\beta
to be arbitrary with the sole restriction that
(\alpha,\beta)\not\equiv(0,0)
mod 1. The corresponding
\zeta
-function is defined by the Kronecker-Eisenstein sum,

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}(\tau,s)=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\tau_{2}^{s}\over\pi^{s}|m+\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau|^{2s}}

(5.9.2)

which is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
. We shall show below that
\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}(\tau,s)
may be analytically continued in
s
throughout the complex plane. The determinant is obtained from this analytic continuation as usual,

\displaystyle\ln{\rm Det}\Delta_{\kappa}=-\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}^{\prime}(\tau,0)

(5.9.3)

We recast the Kronecker-Eisenstein sum in terms of the following heat-kernel representation,

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}(\tau,s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\exp\left\{-{\pi t\over\tau_{2}}\big|m+\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau\big|^{2}\right\}

(5.9.4)

and Poisson re-sum either in the summation variable
m
if
\beta\not=0
or in
n
if
\alpha\not=0
(but not in both). We shall work out the case
\beta\not=0
, the other case being analogous, using the formula,

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-\pi t|m+\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau|^{2}/\tau_{2}}=\sqrt{{\tau_{2}\over t}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{2\pi im(\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau_{1}}\,e^{-\pi m^{2}\tau_{2}/t-\pi(n+\beta)^{2}t\tau_{2}}

(5.9.5)

Substituting this result into (
5.9.4
) and splitting the sum over
m,n
into contributions for
m\not=0
and
m=0
, we have
\Gamma(s)\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}(\tau,s)=A_{s}(\tau)+B_{s}(\tau)
with,

\displaystyle A_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sum_{m\not=0}\sum_{n}e^{2\pi im(\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau_{1})}\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s-{1\over 2}}\,e^{-\pi\tau_{2}(m^{2}/t+(n+\beta)^{2}t)}

\displaystyle B_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt\over t}\,t^{s-{1\over 2}}\sum_{n}\,e^{-\pi(n+\beta)^{2}t\tau_{2}}

(5.9.6)

The integral
A_{s}(\tau)
is manifestly holomorphic in
s
throughout
{\mathbb{C}}
, and may be evaluated by performing the
t
-integral in terms of a Bessel function,

\displaystyle A_{s}(\tau)=2\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sum_{m\not=0}\sum_{n}\left|{m\over n+\beta}\right|^{s-{1\over 2}}e^{2\pi im(\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau_{1})}K_{s-{1\over 2}}\big(2\pi\tau_{2}|m(n+\beta)|\big)

(5.9.7)

To evaluate the determinant, we only need
A_{0}(\tau)
, which may be read off from the above formula by setting
s=0

and using the expression for the spherical Bessel
K_{-{1\over 2}}(x)=\sqrt{\pi/2x}\,e^{-x}
,

\displaystyle A_{0}(\tau)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n}{1\over m}\,e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}m|n+\beta|}\left(e^{2\pi im(\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau_{1})}+e^{-2\pi im(\alpha+(n+\beta)\tau_{1})}\right)

(5.9.8)

Without loss of generality we may take
0<\beta<1
to carry out the sum and obtain,

\displaystyle A_{0}(\tau)=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\ln\left|1-q^{n+\beta}\,e^{2\pi i\alpha}\right|^{2}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\ln\left|1-q^{n-\beta}\,e^{-2\pi i\alpha}\right|^{2}

(5.9.9)

Finally, we compute
B_{s}(\tau)
by performing the integrals under the sum,

\displaystyle B_{s}(\tau)=\Gamma(s-\tfrac{1}{2})\pi^{{1\over 2}-s}\tau_{2}^{1-s}\sum_{n}{1\over|n+\beta|^{2s-1}}

(5.9.10)

The sum is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
and may be evaluated in terms of Hurwitz
\zeta
-functions. The analytic continuation of the Hurwitz
\zeta
-function is well-known, and the final result is given by,

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Delta_{\kappa}}^{\prime}(\tau,0)=-\ln\left|{\vartheta[\alpha\,\beta](0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}

(5.9.11)

This result is a version of the second Kronecker limit formula.

The determinant obtained above is for the
bc
system in which both
b
and
c
are conformal spinor fields of weight
(\tfrac{1}{2},0)
, along with their complex conjugate fields. The natural candidate for the determinant of the chiral operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
is obtained by retaining only the part with holomorphic dependence on
\tau
,

\displaystyle{\rm Det}\,\partial_{\bar{z}}\Big|_{bc}={\vartheta[\alpha\,\beta](0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}

(5.9.12)

The spinor fields that are required in string theory for the worldsheet matter fermions are further restricted to
\psi=b=c
, so that the
b
and
c
fields are actually identical fields. The functional chiral determinant of the operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}
in this case is the square root of the determinant of the
bc
system,

\displaystyle{\rm Det}\,\partial_{\bar{z}}\Big|_{\psi}={\vartheta[\alpha\,\beta](0|\tau)^{1\over 2}\over\eta(\tau)^{1\over 2}}

(5.9.13)

Neither determinant is modular invariant or even covariant. Physicists say that there is a global anomaly, which manifests the incompatibility between maintaining holomorphicity and modular invariance. We may try a combination similar to the one we established in the non-chiral case,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{4}{\vartheta_{i}(0|\tau)^{{N\over 2}}\over\eta(\tau)^{{N\over 2}}}={\vartheta[00](0|\tau)^{{N\over 2}}\over\eta(\tau)^{{N\over 2}}}+{\vartheta[0{1\over 2}](0|\tau)^{{N\over 2}}\over\eta(\tau)^{{N\over 2}}}+{\vartheta[{1\over 2}0](0|\tau)^{{N\over 2}}\over\eta(\tau)^{{N\over 2}}}

(5.9.14)

The smallest value of
N
for which we have good modular transformations is
N=16
, and more generally any multiple of
16
. In those cases, the sum of the numerators transforms as a modular form of weight
N/4
.
The first modular invariant is encountered as
N=24
for 48 chiral fermions.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

A systematic introduction to quantum field theory and more specifically conformal field theory in two dimensions is presented in the book by Di Francesco, Mathieu, and Sénéchal
[
47
]
, which also has an extensive bibliography. The lecture notes by Ginsparg
[
48
]
provide an excellent introduction.

The development of two-dimensional conformal field theory and the use of radial quantization date back to
[
49
]
. Modern conformal field theory, based on the representation theory of the Virasoro algebra, was developed in
[
50
]
, where minimal models were also introduced. The
bc
system was discussed in the context of string perturbation theory in
[
51
]
. Functional determinants of Laplace and Cauchy-Riemann operators on the torus are evaluated there as well as in the classic paper by Polchinski
[
52
]
. The Kronecker limit formulas are discussed in detail in the lecture notes by Siegel
[
53
]
, as well as in
[
22
]
.

On higher genus Riemann surfaces, functional determinants of Laplace operators were considered in
[
54
]
in connection with Ray-Singer torsion, in
[
55
]
, and in
[
56
]
in connection with Reidemeister torsion, and were evaluated in terms of the Selberg zeta function in
[
57
,
58
]
. An overview is presented in the booklet by Jorgensen and Lang
[
59
]
. Functional determinants of Laplace and Cauchy-Riemann operators were evaluated in terms of Riemann
\vartheta
-functions via chiral bosonization in
[
60
]
and
[
61
]
by building on the properties of the holomorphic anomaly established in
[
62
]
and
[
63
]
.

6

Congruence subgroups and modular curves

The modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
possesses an infinite number of non-Abelian subgroups. Various objects encountered in the previous sections, such as the Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions,
\vartheta
-constants, and the Dedekind
\eta
-function, do not transform as modular forms under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but they are modular forms under certain subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. These subgroups belong to the general class of congruence subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, which we shall now define and study.

6.1

Definition of congruence subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

The principal congruence subgroup of level
N\geq 1
is defined as follows,

\displaystyle\Gamma(N)=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right),~~a,d\equiv 1~({\rm mod}\,N)\,,~~b,c\equiv 0~({\rm mod}\,N)\right\}

(6.1.1)

Clearly, we have
\Gamma(1)=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\Gamma(N)
is a subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
for any
N
. Actually,
\Gamma(N)
is a normal subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
so that the quotient is itself a group. The quotient group is isomorphic to
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
(to physicists, isomorphism is synonymous with equality),

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma(N)=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})

(6.1.2)

namely the group of
2\times 2
matrices whose entries are integers mod
N
and whose determinant is 1 mod
N
.
Equivalently, the group
\Gamma(N)
may be identified with the kernel of the homomorphism
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
of reduction mod
N
. The index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(N)]
of
\Gamma(N)
equals the order of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
, which is finite. It will be determined below shortly.

By definition, a group
\Gamma
is a congruence subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of level
N
provided there exists an integer
N\geq 1
such that
\Gamma(N)\subset\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Since the index of
\Gamma(N)
is finite, it follows that the index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]
is finite for any congruence subgroup
\Gamma
. Note
that
\Gamma
need not be a normal subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
so that
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma
need not be a group.

6.2

The classic congruence subgroups

Besides the principal congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N)
, two other fundamental congruence subgroups of level
N
are defined as follows,
15
15

15

The groups of transposes are denoted by,

\Gamma^{1}(N)=\{\gamma|\gamma^{t}\in\Gamma_{1}(N)\}
and

\Gamma^{0}(N)=\{\gamma|\gamma^{t}\in\Gamma_{0}(N)\}
.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right),~~a,d\equiv 1~({\rm mod}\,N)\,,~~c\equiv 0~({\rm mod}\,N)\right\}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right),~~c\equiv 0~({\rm mod}\,N)\right\}

(6.2.1)

with the following manifest subgroup inclusions,

\displaystyle\Gamma(N)\subset\Gamma_{1}(N)\subset\Gamma_{0}(N)\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(6.2.2)

For
N\geq 2
, none of the groups
\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N),\Gamma_{0}(N)
contains the generator
S
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
and, while
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
contain the translation generator
T
, the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
contains
T^{N}
but not
T
.

We recall that
\Gamma(N)
is a normal subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
induced by the homomorphism
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
on all its entries. Similarly,
\Gamma(N)
is a normal subgroup of
\Gamma_{1}(N)
induced by
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
on the entry
b
whose quotient is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
. Finally,
\Gamma_{1}(N)
is a normal subgroup of
\Gamma_{0}(N)
whose quotient is isomorphic to the group
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}
of all multiplicatively invertible elements in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
. In summary, we have the following quotients and isomorphisms,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(N)/\Gamma(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}(N)/\Gamma_{1}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}

(6.2.3)

The order of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
is denoted
2d_{N}
and will be evaluated in section
6.3
. The order of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is
N
. The order of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}
equals the Euler function
\phi(N)
, which counts the number of integers in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
that are relatively prime to
N
, and is given by the following formula,

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}|=\phi(N)=N\prod_{i}\left(1-{1\over p_{i}}\right)\hskip 72.26999ptN=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{e_{i}}~\hbox{ for }~e_{i}\geq 1

(6.2.4)

where the products are over distinct primes
p_{i}
. For the special case where
N
is prime, we have
\phi(N)=N-1
. The indices of these subgroups are related to the orders of the quotient groups as follows,

\displaystyle{}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(N)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|=2d_{N}

\displaystyle{}[\Gamma_{1}(N):\Gamma(N)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}|=N

\displaystyle{}[\Gamma_{0}(N):\Gamma_{1}(N)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}|=\phi(N)

(6.2.5)

We obtain the remaining indices,

\displaystyle{}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma_{0}(N)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2d_{N}/(N\phi(N))

\displaystyle{}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma_{1}(N)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2d_{N}/N

(6.2.6)

using the multiplicative property
[G:H]\times[H:K]=[G:K]
for groups
K\subset H\subset G
.

6.3

Computing the order of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})

We shall now evaluate the order
2d_{N}
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
, following
[
13
]
. The order will enter crucially in the valence formulas for congruence subgroups. To compute
d_{N}
, we shall make use of the Chinese remainder theorem (reviewed and proven in appendix
A
), which provides a fundamental isomorphism for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
in terms of its prime decomposition factors.

Let
N
have the following prime number decomposition
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{e_{i}}
where the
p_{i}
are distinct primes and
e_{i}
are positive integer exponents. Then by the Chinese remainder theorem, we have the following isomorphism,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})=\prod_{i}SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p_{i}^{e_{i}}})

(6.3.1)

Thus, the order
|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|
of the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
is given by,

\displaystyle|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|=\prod_{i}|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p_{i}^{e_{i}}})|

(6.3.2)

To compute the order of each component
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}})
we proceed by induction on
e
. We begin by computing the order of
GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. To do so, consider the set of all matrices
\gamma
,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.3.3)

whose entries
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
may be parametrized by
a,b,c,d\in\{0,1,\cdots,p-1\}
. To belong to
GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, the entries must satisfy
ad-bc\not=0~({\rm mod~}p)
. To enumerate all its elements we proceed by listing the number of elements in the four subsets of possible values of the doublet
(a,b)
. No matrices with
a=b=0
belong to
GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
; there are
p-1
doublets with
a\not=0,b=0
and for each non-zero value of
a
the
p-1
non-zero values of
d
and the
p
arbitrary values
c
give
(p-1)^{2}p
elements

\gamma\in GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. For the case
a=0,b\not=0
the roles of
c
and
d
are reversed and we obtain another
(p-1)^{2}p
elements. Finally, for each one of the
(p-1)^{2}
doublets with
a,b\not=0
, the entry
c
may take all
p
values, while
d
may take all
p
values except the one that gives zero determinant, producing
(p-1)^{3}p
contributions. Putting all together, we find,

\displaystyle|GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})|=(p-1)^{3}p+2(p-1)^{2}p=(p-1)p^{3}\left(1-{1\over p^{2}}\right)

(6.3.4)

Any
\gamma\in GL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
satisfies
{\rm det\,}\gamma\not=0
, and taking the quotient by the determinant normal subgroup isomorphic to the multiplicative group
({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{*}
with
p-1
elements, we obtain,

\displaystyle|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})|=p^{3}\left(1-{1\over p^{2}}\right)

(6.3.5)

To proceed by induction on the exponent
e
, we consider an arbitrary matrix
\gamma^{\prime}

({\rm mod~}p^{e+1})
and parametrize it uniquely
({\rm mod~}p^{e})
,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma+p^{e}\left(\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}\cr c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.3.6)

where
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}}
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
. Requiring
{\rm det\,}\gamma\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}p^{e+1})

requires
ad-bc\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}p^{e})
so that
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}})
. Now for an arbitrary matrix
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}})
, we obtain
\gamma^{\prime}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e+1}})
if and only if,

\displaystyle ad^{\prime}+da^{\prime}-bc^{\prime}-cb^{\prime}\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)

(6.3.7)

At least two entries in
\gamma
are non-zero
({\rm mod~}p)
since
ad-bc\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}p)
. If
a\not=0
, we let
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}
take independent arbitrary values in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
and determine the number
d^{\prime}
uniquely by solving equation (
6.3.7
). If
a=0
, then we must have
b\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
, take the values of
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},d^{\prime}
arbitrary and solve uniquely for
c^{\prime}
using (
6.3.7
). In each case, to every
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}})
there correspond
p^{3}
elements
\gamma^{\prime}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e+1}})
. Hence we have,

\displaystyle|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{p^{e}})|=p^{3e}\left(1-{1\over p^{2}}\right)

(6.3.8)

Putting all together, for any
N\geq 2
with prime number decomposition
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{e_{i}}
we have,

\displaystyle 2d_{N}=|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|=N^{3}\prod_{i}\left(1-{1\over p_{i}^{2}}\right)

(6.3.9)

The order of the simplest non-trivial group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is 6 by the above formula. In fact, we can write its generators in terms of the generators
S
and
T
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
and we find,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=\big\{I,\,S,\,T,\,ST,\,TS,\,STS\big\}\quad({\rm mod~}2)

(6.3.10)

Opposite elements are not included in the set since
I\equiv-I~({\rm mod~}2)
.

6.4

Modular curves

Recall that the quotient
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}
of the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
by
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is topologically a sphere with one puncture corresponding to the unique cusp. The quotient
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash{\cal H}
may be represented by a choice of fundamental domain in
{\cal H}
with boundary sides identified pairwise under generators of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The standard choice
F
of fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
was defined in (
3.2.6
) and depicted in Figure
6
.

For an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
the quotient is the modular curve
Y(\Gamma)=\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}
which is again a Riemann surface. However,
Y(\Gamma)
may be a sphere or a surface of genus greater than zero and may have several punctures corresponding to the several cusps of
\Gamma
.

6.4.1

Fundamental domain for a congruence subgroup
\Gamma

The modular curve
Y(\Gamma)
may again be represented by a choice of fundamental domain
F_{\Gamma}
in
{\cal H}
with opposite sides identified pairwise under generators of
\Gamma
. Given the fundamental domain
F
for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, the fundamental domain
F_{\Gamma}
may be obtained by applying the cosets of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma
to
F
,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma:F\to F_{\Gamma}

(6.4.1)

Therefore
F_{\Gamma}
is the union of a number of copies of
F
equal to the index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]
.
The elliptic points of
\Gamma
are the images of the elliptic points
i
and
\rho
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
under the cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma
and, similarly, the cusps of
\Gamma
are the images of the cusp
i\infty
of
F
under the cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma
. Thus, the number of cusps in
Y(\Gamma)
equals the index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]
. For a non-trivial congruence subgroup
\Gamma\not=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, the index is strictly greater than 1, as may be verified for the index for
\Gamma=\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N),\Gamma_{0}(N)
evaluated in (
6.2
) and (
6.2
).
Since
Y(\Gamma)
has at least one puncture, it is always non-compact.

There is a useful alternative way of looking at the cusps of a congruence subgroup
\Gamma
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Recall that the Borel subgroup
\Gamma_{\infty}
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is defined by
\Gamma_{\infty}=\{\pm T^{n},n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
and that
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}
acts transitively on
{\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{\infty\}
. This property results from the fact that every rational number
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
is the image of
i\infty
under a unique coset in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}
. To show this, we distinguish two cases:
r=0
is the image of
i\infty
under the transformation
S\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}
, while for
r\not=0
we write
r=a/c
uniquely with
a,c\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
,
\gcd(a,c)=1
, and
c>0
. By Bézout’s theorem (reviewed in appendix
A
) there exists a particular solution
(b_{0},d_{0})
for
b
and
d
to the equation
ad-bc=1
. The general solution is given by
b=b_{0}+ka
and
d=d_{0}+kc
, which is equivalent to the particular solution by right multiplication by
T^{k}
, which proves the assertion.
The cusps of
Y(\Gamma)
may now be viewed as those points in
{\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{\infty\}
which are inequivalent under the action of the congruence group
\Gamma
, or equivalently,
16
16

16

Throughout, it will be convenient to use the notation
\Gamma z=\{\gamma z\hbox{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma\}
for the set of all images of the point
z
under the action of the group
\Gamma
.

\displaystyle\{\hbox{cusps of }\Gamma\}=\big(\Gamma\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}\big)\,\{\infty\}

(6.4.2)

The number of cusps, counted with multiplicity, is again given by the index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]
.

For the example
\Gamma=\Gamma(2)
with
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma(2)=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
, the fundamental domain
F_{\Gamma(2)}
is the union of the 6 images of
F
under the elements of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
listed in (
6.3.10
). The resulting fundamental domain
F_{\Gamma(2)}
is depicted in Figure
8
. Using the area formula (
3.2.4
) we have
{\rm area}(F_{\Gamma(2)})=2\pi
or
{\rm area}(F_{\Gamma(2)})=6\,{\rm area}(F)
, given by the order of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
, as expected. The
\Gamma(2)
-inequivalent elliptic points in
F_{\Gamma(2)}
are
i,i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},i^{\prime\prime\prime},\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and its
\Gamma(2)
-inequivalent cusps are
0,1,i\infty
. The elliptic points
i
and
i^{\prime}
have multiplicity 2;
i^{\prime\prime}
and
i^{\prime\prime\prime}
have multiplicity 1;
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
have multiplicity 6, while the cusps
0,1,i\infty
all have multiplicity 2.

for the congruence subgroup

, its elliptic points

, and its cusps
0,\pm 1,i\infty
are obtained as the images of the fundamental domain
F
, the elliptic points
i,\rho
, and the cusp
