of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, respectively, under the action of the group of cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=\{I,S,T,ST,TS,STS\}
.

6.5

Compactification of the modular curves

To compactify
Y(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}))
, one adjoins the point
\{i\infty\}
to the fundamental domain
F
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. To compactify
Y(\Gamma)
for an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
, one similarly adjoins
\{\hbox{cusp of }\Gamma\}=\Gamma\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}\,\{i\infty\}
to
Y(\Gamma)
. For an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
, the resulting modular curve
X(\Gamma)
is a connected and compact Riemann surface.

An alternative but equivalent way to obtain the compact modular curve
X(\Gamma)
is to start from the union
{\cal H}^{*}={\cal H}\cup\{i\infty\}\cup{\mathbb{Q}}
of the upper half-plane with the rational numbers and infinity. One chooses a topology for
{\cal H}^{*}
whose open sets include all the standard open sets of
{\cal H}
(which are generated by the open coordinate discs whose closure lies entirely in
{\cal H}
), as well as the open sets generated by discs that are open in
{\cal H}
and intersect
{\mathbb{R}}
at a rational number. The latter provide an open neighborhood for every point in
\{i\infty\}\cup{\mathbb{Q}}
and are depicted in green in Figure
9
for the discs at infinity
{\cal U}_{i\infty}
and the discs
{\cal U}_{\pm 1}
and
{\cal U}_{0}
intersecting the real axis at the rational points
\pm 1
and
0
. The topological space
{\cal H}^{*}
obtained this way is not Haussdorff. Recall that a topological space is Haussdorff if for any two distinct points of
{\cal H}^{*}
there exist neighborhoods of each point that are disjoint from each other.

i\infty
-
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
0
-1
1
{\cal H}
{\mathbb{R}}
i
{\cal U}_{i\infty}
{\cal U}_{-1}
{\cal U}_{0}
{\cal U}_{1}

Figure 9
:
The green rectangle
{\cal U}_{i\infty}
represents a neighborhood of the cusp
i\infty
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
while the green discs
{\cal U}_{-1},{\cal U}_{0},{\cal U}_{1}
represent the neighborhoods of the additional cusps of
\Gamma(2)
.

The modular curve
X(\Gamma)
may be obtained from
{\cal H}^{*}
by taking the extended quotient, which includes the action of
\Gamma
on
{\mathbb{Q}}\cup\{i\infty\}
,

\displaystyle X(\Gamma)=\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}^{*}

(6.5.1)

The modular curve
X(\Gamma)
is Haussdorff even though
{\cal H}^{*}
is not. The compact modular curves for the classic congruence subgroups are denoted as follows,

\displaystyle X(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X\big(\Gamma(N)\big)

\displaystyle X_{1}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X\big(\Gamma_{1}(N)\big)

\displaystyle X_{0}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X\big(\Gamma_{0}(N)\big)

(6.5.2)

A modular form
f(\tau)
of weight
k
for a congruence subgroup
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
corresponds to a
\Gamma
-invariant differential form
\mathfrak{f}=f(\tau)d\tau^{{k\over 2}}
on
{\cal H}^{*}
, and thus pulls back to a well-defined differential on
X(\Gamma)=\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}^{*}
.
To obtain the dimensions of the spaces
{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma)
and
{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma)
of modular forms and cusp forms of weight
k
, we can now use the machinery of line bundles on the compact Riemann surfaces
X(\Gamma)
, reviewed in appendix
C
.

6.6

The genus of
X(\Gamma)

To study
X(\Gamma)
, we shall view it as a branched covering of the sphere
X(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}))
by a non-constant holomorphic function
f:X(\Gamma)\to X(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}))
. We provide a summary of various relevant topological aspects of Riemann surfaces in appendix
B
.

6.6.1

The Hurwitz formula for branched coverings

Consider a non-constant holomorphic map
f:X\to Y
between two compact Riemann surfaces
X,Y
. The degree of
f
is the cardinality of
f^{-1}
denoted
d=|f^{-1}(y)|
for all but finitely many points
y\in Y
. A formula valid for all points in
Y
is obtained in terms of the degree of ramification
e_{x}
of
f
at a point
x\in X
which, in local coordinates, is given by
f(z)-f(x)\sim(z-x)^{e_{x}}
. The degree of
f
may then be defined for all
y\in Y
by the formula,

\displaystyle d=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}e_{x}

(6.6.1)

valid for an arbitrary point
y\in Y
.

Theorem 6.1

The Riemann-Hurwitz formula relates the genera
g_{X}
and
g_{Y}
of
X
and
Y
to the degree of
f
and the deviations from 1 of the degree of ramification,

\displaystyle 2g_{X}-2=d(2g_{Y}-2)+\sum_{x\in X}(e_{x}-1)

(6.6.2)

A proof of the Riemann-Hurwitz formula is given in appendix
B
.

6.6.2

Calculating the genus of
X(\Gamma)

Returning now to the map
f:X(\Gamma)\to X(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}))
we obtain an alternative formula for its degree in terms of the index of the congruence subgroup
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle d=\left\{\begin{matrix}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]&\hbox{ if }&-I\in\Gamma\cr\tfrac{1}{2}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma]&\hbox{ if }&-I\not\in\Gamma\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(6.6.3)

We shall denote the elliptic points of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of order
2h
by
y_{h}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\{e^{\pi i/h}\}
for
h=2,3
and the cusps by
y_{\infty}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\{i\infty\}
. The number of elliptic points of order
2h
in
X(\Gamma)
will be denoted by
\varepsilon_{h}
, and the number of cusps in
X(\Gamma)
by
\varepsilon_{\infty}
.

Theorem 6.2

The genus
g
of
X(\Gamma)
is given by,

\displaystyle g=1+{d\over 12}-{\varepsilon_{2}\over 4}-{\varepsilon_{3}\over 3}-{\varepsilon_{\infty}\over 2}

(6.6.4)

where
d
is given in (
6.6.3
),
\varepsilon_{h}
denotes the number of elliptic points of order
2h
, and
\varepsilon_{\infty}
denotes the number of cusps in
X(\Gamma)
.

To prove this result using the Riemann-Hurwitz formula (
6.6.2
), we evaluate the contributions from the points
x\in X(\Gamma)
for which
e_{x}>1
, namely from the points
f^{-1}(y_{h})
and the cusps of
X(\Gamma)
. If
f^{-1}(y_{h})
is a ramification point of
X(\Gamma)
then
e_{y_{h}}=1
, while if it is not a ramification point of
X(\Gamma)
then
e_{y_{h}}=h
. Using the degree formula (
B.1.3
) at the points
y_{h}
, we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{h})}e_{x}=\varepsilon_{h}+h(|f^{-1}(y_{h})|-\varepsilon_{h})

(6.6.5)

where the first term arises from those points
y_{h}
that are ramification points of
X(\Gamma)
while the second term arises from those points in
f^{-1}(y_{h})
that are not ramification points of
X(\Gamma)
, in each case weighted by their respective ramification degrees 1 and
h
. Using the degree formula again to eliminate the number
|f^{-1}(y_{h})|
, one obtains,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{h})}(e_{x}-1)={h-1\over h}(d-\varepsilon_{h})

(6.6.6)

The formula holds for the cusps by setting
h=\infty
. Using the Riemann-Hurwitz formula (
6.6.2
), we set
g_{Y}=0
,
g=g_{X(\Gamma)}
, and substitute the contributions to the sum of
(e_{x}-1)
obtained in (
6.6.6
) to obtain (
6.6.4
).

6.7

Formulas for
d
,
\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\infty}
for
\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)

In order to obtain formulas for the genus of
X(\Gamma)
, and later on the dimensions of the spaces of modular and cusp forms on
X(\Gamma)
, we need to obtain the degree
d
, and formulas for
\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{\infty}
for an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
. But first, it will be useful to obtain these formulas for the special cases of the congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N)
, and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. To do so, we begin by assembling all the partial results that are required.

6.7.1

The absence of elliptic points

Recall that the elliptic points of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
are the points of order four, namely
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\{i\}
, and the points of order six, namely
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\{e^{2\pi i/6}\}
. In this subsection and the next, we shall count the number of elliptic points of orders four and six of the congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N)
, and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. We begin with the following theorem.

Theorem 6.3

The following congruence groups have no elliptic points,

1.

\Gamma(N)
for
N\geq 2
;

2.

\Gamma_{1}(N)
for
N\geq 4
;

3.

\Gamma_{0}(N)
for any
N
divisible by a prime
p
which obeys
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}12)
.

To prove 1, we parametrize
\gamma\in\Gamma(N)
as follows,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}1+Na^{\prime}&Nb^{\prime}\cr Nc^{\prime}&1+Nd^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.1)

For an elliptic
\gamma
we must have
|a+d|<2
, following the analysis of subsection
3.2.3
. The condition
|a+d|=|2+N(a^{\prime}+d^{\prime})|<2
allows for three possibilities, namely
N(a^{\prime}+d^{\prime})=-1,-2,-3
, which are all ruled out for all
N\geq 4
. For both
N=2
and
N=3
, the only solution is given by
a^{\prime}+d^{\prime}=-1
. Combining this condition with
{\rm det\,}\gamma=1
, we find that we must have
N(a^{\prime}d^{\prime}-b^{\prime}c^{\prime})=1
which has no solutions for
N\geq 2
. To prove 2, we parametrize
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}(N)
as follows,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}1+Na^{\prime}&b\cr Nc^{\prime}&1+Nd^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.2)

The condition
|a+d|=|2+N(a^{\prime}+d^{\prime})|<2
for
\gamma
to be elliptic again allows for three possibilities, namely
N(a^{\prime}+d^{\prime})=-1,-2,-3
, which are all ruled out for
N\geq 4
. To prove 3, we parametrize
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(N)
as follows,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr Nc^{\prime}&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.3)

The condition
|a+d|<2
again leaves three possibilities, namely
a+d=0,\pm 1
. For
a+d=0
, we furthermore have

-a^{2}-Nbc^{\prime}=1
, so that
a^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
. For
a+d=\pm 1
, we have
-a^{2}\pm a-Nbc^{\prime}=1
so that
a^{2}\pm a\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
. Now if
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}12)
then
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}3)
and
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}4)
. But
a^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}p)
has no solutions if
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}4)
in view of (
A.4.6
) while
a^{2}+a\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}p)
has no solutions in view (
A.4.8
), which completes the proof of point 3 and of the theorem.

6.7.2

Counting elliptic points for
\Gamma_{0}(N)

We now count elliptic points for
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, at the values of
N
for which they exist. There are two cases, namely elliptic points of orders 4 and 6, for which the transformation in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is conjugate to either,

\displaystyle S=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\cr 1&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999ptST=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\cr 1&1\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.4)

with
S^{4}=(ST)^{6}=I
,
{\rm tr}(S)=0
, and
{\rm tr}(ST)=1
. Given the conditions on the traces, the representatives of
S
and
ST
in
\Gamma_{0}(N)
are of the form,

\displaystyle\gamma_{4}=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr Nc^{\prime}&-a\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma_{6}=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr Nc^{\prime}&1-a\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.5)

with
-a^{2}-bc^{\prime}N=1
and
a-a^{2}-bc^{\prime}N=1
respectively. We shall now assume the prime decomposition
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
for distinct primes
p_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}\geq 1
.
For the elliptic points of order 4 the congruence equation mod
N
,

\displaystyle a^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)

(6.7.6)

is equivalent to the congruence equations
a^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})
. Using the results of subsection
A.4.1
the number of solutions is given by (
A.4.6
),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&&4|N\cr\prod_{i}\Big(1+(-1|p_{i})\Big)&&4\nmid N\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(6.7.7)

where
(-1|p)=\pm 1
for
p\equiv\pm 1~({\rm mod~}4)
and vanishes for
p=2
.
For the elliptic points of order 6, the congruence equation,
a^{2}+a\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
is equivalent to,

\displaystyle(2a+1)^{2}+3\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}4N)

(6.7.8)

The number of solutions was obtained in subsection
A.4.2
, and is given by,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}(\Gamma_{0}(N))=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&&9|N\cr\prod_{i}\Big(1+(-3|p_{i})\Big)&&9\nmid N\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(6.7.9)

where
(-3|p)=\pm 1
for
p\equiv\pm 1~({\rm mod~}3)
and vanishes for
p=3
.

6.7.3

Counting the number of cusps

The cusps of a modular curve
X(\Gamma)
for a congruence subgroup
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
are given by the cusps
\{\infty\}\cup{\mathbb{Q}}=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\infty
modulo equivalence under
\Gamma
. In this subsection, we shall count the number
\varepsilon_{\infty}
of cusps of
X(\Gamma)
for
\Gamma=\Gamma(N),\Gamma_{1}(N)
, and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
.

We begin by considering the case
\Gamma=\Gamma(N)
. Two cusps
r=m/n
and
r^{\prime}=m^{\prime}/n^{\prime}
with
\gcd(m,n)=\gcd(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})=1
are equivalent under
\Gamma(N)
provided there exists an
\gamma\in\Gamma(N)
such that
r^{\prime}=\gamma r
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle{m^{\prime}\over n^{\prime}}={(1+Na^{\prime})m+Nb^{\prime}n\over Nc^{\prime}m+(1+Nd^{\prime})n}\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}1+Na^{\prime}&Nb^{\prime}\cr Nc^{\prime}&1+Nd^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)

(6.7.10)

The equation on the left side is equivalent to
mn^{\prime}\equiv m^{\prime}n~({\rm mod~}N)
, whose solution is given by
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})=\pm(m,n)~({\rm mod~}N)
in view of the fact that both pairs
(m,n)
and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
are relatively prime. Therefore, the cusps for
\Gamma(N)
are labelled by all pairs
(m,n)~({\rm mod~}N)
with
\gcd(m,n)=1
.

An alternative perspective on this problem uses double cosets and will lend itself more directly to the cases
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. Double cosets will be used in the construction of Hecke operators in section
10
, where a more extensive discussion will be provided. Suffice it here to say that if
G
is a group and
H_{1},H_{2}
are subgroups of
G
, then a double coset is an orbit under the left action of
H_{1}
and the right action of
H_{2}
containing a given element
g\in G
, and is of the form
H_{1}gH_{2}
. The set of all double cosets is denoted,

\displaystyle H_{1}\backslash G/H_{2}=\{H_{1}\,g\,H_{2},~g\in G\}

(6.7.11)

Applying the construction to computing the cusps of
\Gamma(N)
, we set
G=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
,
H_{1}=\Gamma(N)
,
H_{2}=\Gamma_{\infty}=\{\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b\cr 0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),b\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
. The simple coset
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}
provides a one-to-one map from
\infty
to all the cusps of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
while the further left cosets identified cusps that are equivalent under
\Gamma(N)
. Hence we have,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma(N))=|\Gamma(N)\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}|

(6.7.12)

Since
\Gamma(N)
is a normal subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\Gamma(N)\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})
, we have,

\displaystyle\Gamma(N)\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})/\bar{\Gamma}_{\infty}

(6.7.13)

where
\bar{\Gamma}_{\infty}=\{\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&b\cr 0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\}
, and therefore the number of cusps is given by,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma(N))=|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|/|\bar{\Gamma}_{\infty}|={d_{N}\over N}

(6.7.14)

in view of (
6.3.9
) and the fact that
|\bar{\Gamma}_{\infty}|=2N
.

The case of an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
may similarly be treated with the double coset construction, and we have,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma)=|\Gamma\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}|

(6.7.15)

The difference with the case of
\Gamma(N)
is that an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
is not a normal subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and this is indeed the case for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. The calculation of the number of cusps in these cases is more involved and we refer to
[
11
,
13
]
for a detailed exposition and here only quote the result,

\displaystyle N\geq 5

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{1}(N))={1\over 2}\sum_{ad=N}\phi(a)\phi(d)

\displaystyle N\geq 3

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{0}(N))=\sum_{ad=N}\phi\big(\gcd(a,d)\big)

(6.7.16)

where the sums are over
a,d>0
and furthermore,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{1}(2))=\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{1}(3))=\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{0}(2))=2\hskip 72.26999pt\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma_{1}(4))=3

(6.7.17)

This concludes our calculation of
d,\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}
and
\varepsilon_{\infty}
, from which the genus of
X(\Gamma)
may be computed using (
6.6.4
).

6.8

Explicit expressions for the genus

The formulas for the genus of
X(\Gamma)
are particularly simple for the cases when no elliptic points occur, as specified in Theorem
6.1
. In these cases the genus is obtained by combining (
6.6.4
) with the formula for the degree in (
6.6.1
) with the expressions of the indices given in (
6.2
), (
6.2
), and the formulas for the number of cusps in (
6.7.14
), (
6.7.3
), (
6.7.17
),

\displaystyle N\geq 3:

\displaystyle g(X(N))=1+{d_{N}(N-6)\over 12N}

(6.8.1)

\displaystyle N\geq 5:

\displaystyle g(X_{1}(N))=1+{d_{N}\over 12N}-{1\over 4}\sum_{ad=N}\phi(a)\phi(d)

\displaystyle N\geq 2:

\displaystyle g(X_{0}(N))=1+{d_{N}\over 6N\phi(N)}-{\varepsilon_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\over 4}-{\varepsilon_{3}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\over 3}-{1\over 2}\sum_{ad=N}\phi\big(\gcd(a,d)\big)

where
\varepsilon_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
and
\varepsilon_{3}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
were given in (
6.7.7
) and (
6.7.9
) respectively. For low values of
N
, we obtain the expressions for
d_{N}
and the genera given in Table
1
.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The books by Shimura
[
11
]
and by Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
stand out for their clear and useful introductions to congruence subgroups and modular curves. Our summary here closely follows their presentation.

7

Modular forms for congruence subgroups

In this section, we shall discuss the structure and the counting of modular forms for an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
, and present the corresponding Eisenstein series for
\Gamma
. These modular forms are maps from the modular curves
X(\Gamma)
to spaces of meromorphic differentials. The modular curves are compact Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus, and the spaces of meromorphic differentials may be viewed as spaces of meromorphic sections of holomorphic line bundles of the Riemann surface. We refer to appendix
C
for a review of holomorphic line bundles on compact Riemann surfaces, the Riemann-Roch theorem, various vanishing theorems, and dimension formulas for the spaces of meromorphic sections. We shall then give, without proof, the general formula for the dimensions of modular forms and cusp forms for arbitrary
\Gamma
and provide concrete examples for the cases of the standard congruence subgroups.

7.1

Modular forms and cusp forms with respect to
\Gamma

One defines modular functions, modular forms, and cusp forms for an arbitrary congruence subgroup
\Gamma
by analogy with
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Let
f(\tau)
be a meromorphic function on the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
which obeys the following transformation law,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(7.1.1)

for some integer
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Since
\Gamma
is a congruence subgroup, there exists an integer
N
such that
\Gamma(N)\subset\Gamma
, and hence a minimum integer
h
which divides
N
such that
T^{h}\in\Gamma
. As a result,
f
is invariant under
\tau\to\tau+h
and admits a Fourier expansion,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{n}q_{h}^{n}\hskip 72.26999ptq_{h}=e^{2\pi i\tau/h}

(7.1.2)

1.

f
is a modular function of weight
k
if
f
is meromorphic at every cusp of
\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}
, namely if the Fourier expansion of
f(\gamma\tau)
has only a finite number of non-vanishing Fourier coefficients
a_{n}
with negative
n
for every
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
;

2.

f
is a modular form of weight
k
if it is holomorphic in
{\cal H}
and at every cusp of
\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}
, namely the Fourier coefficients
a_{n}
of
f(\gamma\tau)
vanish for all negative
n
for every
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and thus
f
is finite at all cups;

3.

f
is a cusp form of weight
k
if it is a modular form and vanishes at all cusps.

The spaces of modular and cusp forms of weight
k
are denoted
{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma)
and
{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma)
respectively. Clearly, the smaller the subgroup, the more modular forms it will have,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma\hskip 36.135pt\left\{\begin{matrix}{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma)\subset{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma^{\prime})\cr{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma)\subset{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma^{\prime})\end{matrix}\right.

(7.1.3)

In particular, a congruence subgroup
\Gamma
of level
N
contains
\Gamma(N)
so that
{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma)\subset{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma(N))
and similarly for cusp forms.

7.1.1

Modular forms of odd weight

For the full modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
there are no non-zero modular forms of odd weight
k
because the transformation law (
7.1.1
) for the element
-I\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
shows that
f(\tau)=0
. The same conclusion holds for any congruence subgroup
\Gamma
that contains
-I
. The congruence subgroups
\Gamma(2),\Gamma_{1}(2)=\Gamma_{0}(2)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
for every
N
contain
-I
, and thus have no modular forms of odd weight
k
, while
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}(N),\Gamma_{0}(N)
for
N>2
do not contain
-I
so that the corresponding spaces
{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma)
and
{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma)
for
k
odd are not necessarily trivial.

7.1.2

Comments on terminology

The terminology for automorphic functions and automorphic forms and its relation with the terminology for modular functions and modular forms is not entirely standard in the Mathematics literature, and may lead to some confusion, which we shall eliminate here with the help of precise definitions.

Automorphic functions and forms may be defined generally in terms of functions with specific transformation properties under a discrete group acting on a complex manifold. Here, we shall limit attention to the case where the complex manifold is the Poincaré upper half-plane
{\cal H}
and the discrete group
\Gamma
is a Fuchsian subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, acting on
{\cal H}
by Möbius transformations (see appendix
B
for the definition of Fuchsian groups).

In the complex analytic category, following Shimura
[
11
]
and Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
, an automorphic function
f
with respect to a Fuchsian group
\Gamma
is a meromorphic function on
{\cal H}
that is invariant under all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=f(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\hbox{ for all }\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(7.1.4)

and is meromorphic at the cusps of
\Gamma
in
{\cal H}
. An automorphic form of weight
k
with respect to
\Gamma
is a meromorphic function on
{\cal H}
that instead transforms under all
\gamma\in\Gamma
with a non-trivial automorphy factor
(c\tau+d)^{k}
,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}f(\tau)

(7.1.5)

Equivalently, we may view an automorphic form
f
of weight
k
as a differential
k/2
-form

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}=f(\tau)(d\tau)^{\tfrac{k}{2}}

(7.1.6)

which is invariant under
\Gamma
, in analogy with the approach taken in subsection
3.4
. The nomenclature of a form is particularly suited here, and familiar to a physics audience.

In the category of non-holomorphic functions, following Terras
[
20
]
, we may define an automorphic function as a complex-valued function of
{\cal H}
that is invariant under
\Gamma
, thereby generalizing the notion from the meromorphic case. From the point of view of a physicist, it is natural to generalize the notion of an automorphic form to the non-holomorphic category by defining an automorphic form of weight
(k,\ell)
to transform under all
\gamma\in\Gamma
by,

\displaystyle f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}(c\bar{\tau}+d)^{\ell}f(\tau)

(7.1.7)

corresponding to a differential form
\mathfrak{f}
of weight
(\tfrac{k}{2},\tfrac{\ell}{2})
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}=f(\tau)(d\tau)^{\tfrac{k}{2}}(d\bar{\tau})^{\tfrac{\ell}{2}}

(7.1.8)

which is strictly invariant under
\Gamma
. In these notes, unless the meaning is clear from the context, we shall add qualifiers, such as holomorphic automorphic forms, meromorphic automorphic forms, or more generally non-holomorphic automorphic forms.

In the special case where
\Gamma=SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
or a congruence subgroup thereof, one refers to modular functions and forms instead of automorphic functions or forms. Again, unless the meaning is clear from the context, these terms will be accompanied by qualifiers, holomorphic modular forms such as the holomorphic Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
, meromorphic modular functions such as the
j(\tau)
-function, meromorphic modular forms such as the inverse discriminant
\Delta(\tau)^{-1}
, and non-holomorphic modular functions such as the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
and modular graph functions.

7.2

Holomorphic Eisenstein series for
\Gamma(N)

The holomorphic Eisenstein series of integer weight
k\geq 3
was defined in (
2.4.5
) by,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\prime}{1\over(m\tau+n)^{k}}

(7.2.1)

where the prime on the sum instructs us to omit any contribution that makes the summand singular, in this case just
(m,n)=(0,0)
. Clearly
G_{k}(\tau)
vanishes for odd
k
, and is a modular form of weight
k
for even
k
, transforming as follows,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}G_{k}(\tau)\hskip 50.58878pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(7.2.2)

The ring of modular forms of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is polynomial and generated by
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
.

To construct modular forms for congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
with
N\geq 2
we parametrize the elements of the group
\Gamma(N)\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})=({\mathbb{Z}}_{N})^{2}
by a row vector
v=(\mu~\nu)
whose entries satisfy
\mu,\nu\in\{0,1,\cdots N-1\}={\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
, and introduce the following Kronecker-Eisenstein sum,
17
17

17

When no confusion is expected to arise we shall sometimes abbreviate
({\rm mod~}N)
as simply
(N)
.

\displaystyle G_{k}(v|\tau)=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\prime}{1\over\big((mN+\mu)\tau+nN+\nu\big)^{k}}=\sum_{(m,n)\equiv v\,(N)}^{\prime}{1\over(m\tau+n)^{k}}

(7.2.3)

For
v\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
none of the denominators vanish and the prime on the sum may be omitted.
The double sum is absolutely convergent for integer
k\geq 3
, while for
k=1,2
it may be defined by the Eisenstein summation convention,

\displaystyle G_{k}(v|\tau)=\sum_{n\equiv\nu\,(N)}^{\prime}{1\over(\mu\tau+n)^{k}}+\sum_{{m\not=0\atop m\equiv\mu\,(N)}}\sum_{n\equiv\nu\,(N)}{1\over(m\tau+n)^{k}}

(7.2.4)

Under a transformation
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we have,

\displaystyle G_{k}(v\gamma|\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}G_{k}(v|\tau)

(7.2.5)

where the ingredients are defined as follows with
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
ad-bc=1
,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 57.81621pt\gamma\tau={a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\hskip 57.81621ptv\gamma=(a\mu+c\nu,b\mu+d\nu)

(7.2.6)

Under the action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
a row vector
v
may be mapped into itself or into different row vector. For example,
(0~0)
maps to itself under any
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, while the row
(0~\nu_{0})
for
\nu_{0}\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
maps to
(\mu~\nu)=(c\nu_{0}~d\nu_{0})~({\rm mod~}N)
. Conversely, given a row
v=(\mu~\nu)
with
\gcd(\gcd(\mu,\nu),N)=1
, then there exists a
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
which maps
v
to
(0,\nu_{0})
with
\nu_{0}=\gcd(\mu,\nu)~({\rm mod~}N)
.

7.2.1

Invariance under
\Gamma(N)

A transformation
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
maps
G_{k}(v|\tau)
to a multiple of itself provided
v
satisfies
v\gamma-v\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle(a-1)\mu+c\nu

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0~({\rm mod~}N)

\displaystyle b\mu+(d-1)\nu

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0~({\rm mod~}N)

(7.2.7)

This condition is realized for an arbitrary
\gamma\in\Gamma(N)
and an arbitrary pair
(\mu~\nu)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{N})^{2}
. A given pair
v=(\mu~\nu)
may be invariant under a subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
that is larger than
\Gamma(N)
. For example
(0~0)
is invariant under all of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. But if we require that all
v\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{N})^{2}
be invariant then the largest invariance group is
\Gamma(N)
. To see this, it suffices to choose
(\mu~\nu)=(1,0)~({\rm mod~}N)
and
(\mu~\nu)=(0,1)~({\rm mod~}N)
to obtain the conditions
a,d\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}N)
and
b,c\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
so that
\gamma\in\Gamma(N)
.

7.2.2

Asymptotics near the cusps

The cusps of
\Gamma(N)
are given by the double coset,

\displaystyle\Gamma(N)\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma_{\infty}

(7.2.8)

acting on
i\infty
, namely by all the images under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of the cusp at infinity modulo equivalence under
\Gamma(N)
. The number of cusps is denoted
\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma(N))
and was given in (
6.7.14
). Since every cusp may be mapped to
\infty
under a suitable
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, under which
G_{k}(v|\tau)
maps to
G_{k}(v\gamma|\gamma\tau)
, it suffices to examine the asymptotics of
G_{k}(v|\tau)
at the cusp
\infty
, the asymptotics at the other cusps being deduced by mapping back with
\gamma^{-1}
.

The asymptotics of
G_{k}(v|\tau)
is qualitatively different for the cases where
\mu\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
or
\mu\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
. For
\mu\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
the limit at the cusp
\tau\to i\infty
vanishes,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}G_{k}(v|\tau)=0

(7.2.9)

Of course,
G_{k}(v|\tau)
will in general be non-vanishing at cusps other than
i\infty
.
If
\mu\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
and
\nu\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
, then the limit at the cusp
\tau\to i\infty
is given by,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to i\infty}G_{k}(v|\tau)=\sum_{n\equiv\nu\,({\rm mod~}N)}{1\over n^{k}}={1\over N^{k}}\Big(\zeta(k,\tfrac{\nu}{N})+(-)^{k}\zeta(k,1-\tfrac{\nu}{N})\Big)

(7.2.10)

It is shown in
[
13
]
that the Eisenstein series form a basis for the Eisenstein space, which is defined to be the quotient,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}\big(\Gamma(N)\big)={\cal M}_{k}\big(\Gamma(N)\big)/{\cal S}_{k}\big(\Gamma(N)\big)

(7.2.11)

where
{\cal M}_{k}(\Gamma(N))
is the vector space of all holomorphic modular forms of weight
k
and
{\cal S}_{k}(\Gamma(N))
is the sub-space of cusp forms. For even
k
, the dimension of the Eisenstein space is given by the number of cusps
\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma(N))
,

\displaystyle\dim{\cal E}_{k}\big(\Gamma(N)\big)=\varepsilon_{\infty}\big(\Gamma(N)\big)

(7.2.12)

with
\varepsilon_{\infty}(\Gamma(N))
given in section
6.7.3
. One may consider linear combinations of the forms
G_{k}(v|\tau)
which produce normalized Eisenstein series for
\Gamma(N)
, defined by,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{k}(v|\tau)=\sum_{(c,d)\equiv v\,(N)\atop\gcd(c,d)=1}{1\over(c\tau+d)^{k}}

(7.2.13)

For even
k
the limit of
{\sf E}_{k}(v|\tau)
vanishes at all the cusps of
\Gamma(N)
except for the cusp corresponding to
v
.

7.3

Holomorphic Eisenstein series for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)

The construction of holomorphic Eisenstein series for arbitrary congruence subgroups uses Dirichlet characters to symmetrize the Eisenstein series for
\Gamma(N)
. As discussed in more detail in appendix
A.8
, a Dirichlet character
\chi_{a}
for
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a map
\chi_{a}:{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
which is periodic (i.e.
\chi_{a}(n)=\chi_{a}(n+a)
), completely multiplicative (i.e.
\chi_{a}(nm)=\chi_{a}(n)\chi_{a}(m)
), and which is non-vanishing only when
a
and the argument
n
are coprime.

For any positive integer
N
and integer
k\geq 3
, we define the set
{\cal C}_{N,k}
of triples
(\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t)
with
\chi_{a_{1}}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}
(primitive) Dirichlet characters and
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{N,k}:=\left\{(\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t)\,\big|\,\chi_{a_{1}}(-1)\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}(-1)=(-1)^{k}\,\,\,\,\mathrm{and}\,\,\,\,ta_{1}a_{2}\,|N\right\}

(7.3.1)

In terms of Dirichlet characters, we also define the generalized divisor sums

\displaystyle\sigma_{m}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}}(n)=\sum_{d|n}\chi_{a_{1}}(n/d)\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}(d)d^{m}

(7.3.2)

as well as the delta function and Dirichlet
L
-series,

\displaystyle\delta(\chi_{a_{1}})=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&&\chi_{a_{1}}=1~,\\
0&&\chi_{a_{1}}\neq 1~,\end{matrix}\right.\hskip 57.81621ptL(\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}(n)\over n^{s}}~

(7.3.3)

In terms of these ingredients, we may then construct the following functions

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{k}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\chi_{a_{2}},t}(q)=\delta(\chi_{1})+{2\over L(\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},1-k)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}}(n)q^{tn}

(7.3.4)

When
\chi_{a_{1}}=\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}=1
and
k\geq 4
, the generalized divisor sums reduce to the usual divisor sums, and the
L
-series reduces to the Riemann-zeta function. In this case the functions
{\sf E}_{k}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\chi_{a_{2}},t}(q)
reduce to the usual Eisenstein series for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
at shifted values of
q
, i.e.
{\sf E}_{k}^{1,1,t}(q)={\sf E}_{k}(q^{t})
. It is clear that for any
t|N
the functions
{\sf E}_{k}(q^{t})
should be Eisenstein series for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
. What is less obvious is the following theorem, which we quote without proof,

Theorem 7.1

The space of weight-
k
holomorphic Eisenstein series for
\Gamma_{1}(N)
is generated by
{\sf E}_{k}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\chi_{a_{2}},t}(q)
for
({\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t})\in{\cal C}_{N,k}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(\Gamma_{1}(N))=\left\langle{\sf E}_{k}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t}\,\,\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,\mathrm{all}\,\,\,\,({\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t})\in{\cal C}_{N,k}\right\rangle

(7.3.5)

for
k\geq 3
. For
k=2
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{2}(\Gamma_{1}(N))=\left\{\begin{matrix}\left\langle{\sf E}_{2}(q)-t{\sf E}_{2}(q^{t})\,\,\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,\mathrm{all}\,\,\,\,t|N\right\rangle&&\chi_{a_{1}}=\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}=1\\
\left\langle{\sf E}_{2}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t}\,\,\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,\mathrm{all}\,\,\,\,({\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t})\in{\cal C}_{N,2}\right\rangle&&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

(7.3.6)

The proof can be found in
[
13
,
64
]
.
Note the qualitative distinction between the cases of
k=2
and
k>2
, which results from the fact that there is no weight-2 modular function for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The first line of (
7.3.6
) is indeed consistent with the triviality of the space of weight-2 modular forms for
N=1
(and hence
\chi_{a_{1}}=\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}=t=1
).

We now briefly discuss the case of
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. First note that if
\chi_{a_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}
are two Dirichlet characters and
A
is a common multiple of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, then it is possible to define a modulo
A
Dirichlet character
\widehat{\chi}_{A}=\chi_{a_{1}}^{(A)}\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}^{(A)}
, where

\displaystyle\chi_{a}^{(A)}(n):=\left\{\begin{matrix}\chi_{a}(n)&&(n,A)=1\\
0&&(n,A)>1\end{matrix}\right.

(7.3.7)

We may now further define the following subgroups of
{\cal E}_{k}(\Gamma_{1}(N))
,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(N,\widehat{\chi}_{N})=\left\langle{\sf E}_{k}^{\chi_{a_{1}},\chi_{a_{2}},t}\,\,\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,\mathrm{all}\,\,\,\,({\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t})\in{\cal C}_{N,k}\,\,\,\,\mathrm{and}\,\,\,\,\chi_{a_{1}}^{(N)}\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}}^{(N)}=\widehat{\chi}_{N}\right\rangle

(7.3.8)

such that
{\cal E}_{k}(\Gamma_{1}(N))=\bigoplus_{\widehat{\chi}_{N}}{\cal E}_{k}(N,\widehat{\chi}_{N})
. The quantity
\widehat{\chi}_{N}
is referred to as the “nebentypus.” The space of modular forms of
\Gamma_{0}(N)
is then simply obtained by restricting to the sector with trivial nebentypus, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))={\cal E}_{k}(N,1)

(7.3.9)

Let us close with a note for the practical researcher. Holomorphic Eisenstein series for various congruence subgroups, along with many of their properties, can be straightforwardly implemented in the computer program SageMath
[
65
]
. For example, the simple code

\displaystyle>\mathtt{E=EisensteinForms(Gamma0(9),4,prec=10)}

\displaystyle>\mathtt{E.eisenstein\_series()}

can be used to output the Fourier expansions to order
O(q^{10})
for all generators of weight-4 Eisenstein series of
\Gamma_{0}(9)
. The triplets
(\chi_{a_{1}},\widetilde{\chi}_{a_{2}},t)
labelling each generator can be further obtained using

\displaystyle>\mathtt{e=E.eisenstein\_series()}

\displaystyle>\mathtt{for\,\,\,e\,\,\,in\,\,\,E.eisenstein\_series():}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathtt{print\,\,\,e.parameters()}

In practice, using such computer algebra programs to generate the spaces of modular forms at a given level can be much simpler than working through the definitions above.

7.4

Jacobi’s theorem on sums of four squares

We now provide a classic application of modular forms for congruence subgroups, here associated with
\Gamma_{0}(4)
. For any positive integer
n
, we denote by
r_{k}(n)
the number of ways in which
n
can be written as the sum of
k
positive, zero, or negative integers squared,

\displaystyle r_{k}(n)=\#\left\{m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}~\hbox{such that}~n=m_{1}^{2}+\cdots+m_{k}^{2}\right\}

(7.4.1)

The generating function for
r_{k}(n)
in powers of
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
is the function
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{k}
,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{k}=\sum_{m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{m_{1}^{2}+\cdots+m_{k}^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{k}(n)q^{n}

(7.4.2)

Note that the argument of
\vartheta
has been taken to be
2\tau
for convenience, so that the expansion is in terms of
q
raised to integer powers. The function
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{k}
is invariant under
\tau\to\tau+1
but it is not invariant under
\tau\to-1/\tau
for
k\not=0
and, therefore, is not a modular form under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. However, it is a modular form under a certain congruence subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. We specialize to the case
k=4
, and propose to prove Jacobi’s theorem,

\displaystyle r_{4}(n)=8\sum_{d\,|\,n,~4\,\nmid\,d}d

(7.4.3)

with the help of modular forms.
The sum is over positive divisors
d
of
n
which are not divisible by 4. Jacobi’s theorem implies the famous theorem of Lagrange that every positive integer can be written as the sum of four squares, but Lagrange did not evaluate the number of ways in which this can be done.

We begin by identifying the modular subgroup under which
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
is a modular form. One generator may be taken to be
T:\tau\to\tau+1
. Under
\tau\to-1/\tau
we have,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|-2/\tau)^{4}=(-i\tau/2)^{2}\vartheta(0|\tau/2)^{4}

(7.4.4)

so that
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
clearly is not a modular form under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Instead, we look for more general modular transformations
\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
under which we obtain good transformation laws. A general
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
may be simplified by using the fact that we have invariance under
T
, and this allows us to set
b=0
. But then we have
ad=1
which is solved by
a=d=1
and
a=d=-1
. These cases are equivalent to one another upon reversal of the sign of
c
, so that we consider the modular transformation,

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}=\gamma\tau={\tau\over c\tau+1}

(7.4.5)

under which we have,

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(0|2\tau^{\prime}\right)^{4}=\left(i\,{c\tau+1\over 2\tau}\right)^{2}\vartheta\left(0\Big|-{c\over 2}-{1\over 2\tau}\right)^{4}

(7.4.6)

The shift by
-c/2
cancels in view of
T
-invariance if and only if
c\equiv 0~({\rm mod}\,4)
, which may be established by recalling that
\vartheta(z|\tau+1)=\vartheta_{2}(z|\tau)
from (
3.8.3
). Assuming now that
c\equiv 0~({\rm mod}\,4)
the remaining argument
-1/(2\tau)
of
\vartheta
is then independent of
c
. Using
\vartheta(0|-1/(2\tau))^{4}=-4\tau^{2}\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|2\tau^{\prime})^{4}=(c\tau+1)^{2}\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}

(7.4.7)

We have now identified two transformations under which
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
transforms as a modular form of weight 2, namely,

\displaystyle T:\tau\to\tau+1\hskip 72.26999pt\tilde{S}:\tau\to{\tau\over 4\tau+1}

(7.4.8)

These transformations may be used to generate the group of their compositions, which is
\Gamma_{0}(4)
.
Therefore
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
is a weight-2 modular form under the congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(4)
. Of course there are no modular forms of weight two under the full modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. So this is a simple example of the fact that, the “smaller” the modular subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is, the larger the number of modular forms it will admit for a given weight.

Now we would like to identify
\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}
with an Eisenstein series whose Fourier expansion we can compute directly in terms of divisor functions
\sigma_{\alpha}(n)
. What are the modular forms of weight two? Let’s start from
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
which is not a modular form under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but rather a modular connection. Since the difference of two connections is a tensor, we investigate how
{\sf E}_{2}(n\tau)
transforms for various values of
n
. Under
T:\tau\to\tau+1
, the form
{\sf E}_{2}(n\tau)
is invariant for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Next, examine its transformation properties under the transformation

\displaystyle\tau\to\tau^{\prime}={\tau\over N\tau+1}

(7.4.9)

Since
{\sf E}_{2}
is a derivative of
\ln\Delta
given by (
4.1.6
), we examine the transformation law of
\Delta
,

\displaystyle\Delta(n\tau^{\prime})=\left(-{N\tau+1\over n\tau}\right)^{12}\Delta\left(-{N\over n}-{1\over n\tau}\right)

(7.4.10)

When
n|N
, the combination
-N/n
in the argument of
\Delta
cancels by
T
-symmetry. Taking the transform of this simplified result, we obtain after some simplifications,

\displaystyle\Delta(n\tau^{\prime})=(N\tau+1)^{12}\Delta(n\tau)

(7.4.11)

Thus, for
n|N
, the function
\Delta(n\tau)
is a weight 12 modular form under
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. Applying this general result to the sum of four squares problem we set
N=4
, so that
\Delta(\tau),\Delta(2\tau),\Delta(4\tau)
are modular forms of
\Gamma_{0}(4)
. Taking the derivatives of the logarithms on both sides, and expressing the result in terms of
{\sf E}_{2}
, we find,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(n\tau^{\prime})=(4\tau+1)^{2}{\sf E}_{2}(n\tau)+{4\over n}\times{12\over 2\pi i}(4\tau+1)

(7.4.12)

It follows that the two linear combinations,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)-2\,{\sf E}_{2}(2\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt{\sf E}_{2}(2\tau)-2\,{\sf E}_{2}(4\tau)

(7.4.13)

are linearly independent modular forms under
\Gamma_{0}(4)
of weight 2. This matches with the results from (
7.3.9
) and Theorem
7.1
.

We can alternatively count the number of modular forms of weight two under
\Gamma_{0}(4)
by the same arguments we used for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The difference is that the fundamental domain for
\Gamma_{0}(4)
is four times larger than the fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, so we would need to derive an extension of our previous result (
3.6.1
) to
\Gamma_{0}(4)
. We shall do so soon, but for the time being will simply invoke Theorem
7.1
to claim that the space has dimension two. The above combinations generate this space completely, and we must have,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}=\alpha\Big({\sf E}_{2}(\tau)-2\,{\sf E}_{2}(2\tau)\Big)+\beta\Big({\sf E}_{2}(2\tau)-2\,{\sf E}_{2}(4\tau)\Big)

(7.4.14)

for some rational coefficients
\alpha,\beta
. Using the
q
-series representation for
{\sf E}_{2}
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(n\tau)=1-24\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(\ell)q^{n\ell}

(7.4.15)

and matching the
q^{0}
and
q^{1}
terms requires
1=-\alpha-\beta
and
1=-3\alpha
respectively so that,

\displaystyle\vartheta(0|2\tau)^{4}=-{1\over 3}\,{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+{4\over 3}\,{\sf E}_{2}(4\tau)=1+8\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(\ell)q^{\ell}-32\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(\ell)q^{4\ell}

(7.4.16)

Jacobi’s theorem (
7.4.3
) now readily follows.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The books by Shimura
[
11
]
and by Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
provide useful introductions for modular forms for congruence subgroups. We also refer to the book by Miyake
[
64
]
and the software data base
[
65
]
. A clear account of Jacobi’s sum of four squares theorem may be found in Apostol’s book
[
8
]
. Our summary closely follows their presentation. Essays geared towards applications to the proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem are collected in
[
66
]
.

8

Modular differential equations and vector-valued

modular forms

In this section, we construct differential equations in the modular parameter
\tau
, and find solutions to these equations in simple cases. The solutions can generically be assembled into “vector-valued modular forms,” which have proven fruitful in recent works in Mathematics and Physics. In fact, we will see that in general the components of a vector-valued modular forms are modular forms for congruence subgroups, discussed in the previous section.

8.1

Modular covariant derivatives

In section
4.1
we encountered the holomorphic function
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
, whose transformation under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is that of a modular connection, as given in (
4.1.3
). We may use this object to define a modular covariant differential operator, acting on modular forms of weight
k
,

\displaystyle D_{k}={1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}-{k\over 12}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)

(8.1.1)

The operator
D_{k}
maps a modular form of weight
k
to a modular form of weight
(k+2)
,

\displaystyle D_{k}:{\cal M}_{k}\rightarrow{\cal M}_{k+2}

(8.1.2)

as was already shown in section
4.1.3
.

Note that the first order differential operators introduced above can be composed to define higher-order modular covariant derivatives,

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}=\prod_{s=1}^{d}D_{k+2s-2}~,\hskip 43.36243pt{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}:{\cal M}_{k}\rightarrow{\cal M}_{k+2d}

(8.1.3)

For example, the first order differential operators on Eisenstein series give,

\displaystyle D_{4}{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)=-{1\over 3}{\sf E}_{6}\hskip 57.81621ptD_{6}{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)=-{1\over 2}{\sf E}_{4}^{2}

(8.1.4)

while for the second order differential operators we have

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(2)}_{4}{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)={1\over 6}{\sf E}_{4}^{2}\hskip 57.81621pt{\cal D}^{(2)}_{6}{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)={1\over 3}{\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}

(8.1.5)

The second order derivatives follow directly from the first order ones. To prove the former, we note that the spaces of weight
6
and
8
modular forms are generated by
{\sf E}_{6}
and
{\sf E}_{4}^{2}
respectively, so the results are fixed up to normalization. To determine the normalization, one can compare the first terms in the
q
-expansion using (
3.5.13
).

8.2

Modular differential equations

Consider now an arbitrary linear differential equation of order
d
in
\tau
on a modular form
f(\tau)
of weight
k
, referred to as a modular invariant differential equation (MDE),

\displaystyle\left[{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}+\sum_{r=1}^{d}h_{r}(\tau){\cal D}^{(d-r)}_{k}\right]f(\tau)=0

(8.2.1)

In the above equation,
h_{r}(\tau)
is an arbitrary meromorphic modular form of weight
2r
and
f(\tau)
has weight
k
. The simplest MDEs are obtained by taking the
h_{r}(\tau)
to be holomorphic, in which case they are given by polynomials in
{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)
and
{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)
.

8.2.1

First order MDE

The generic first order MDE for modular weight
k
takes the form,

\displaystyle D_{k}f+h_{1}f=0

(8.2.2)

for a modular function
h_{1}
of weight 2 and a modular function
f
of weight
k
. Using the relation,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)={1\over 2\pi i}\partial_{\tau}\ln\Delta(\tau)

(8.2.3)

we obtain a simplified equation for the modular function
f_{0}
defined by,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\Delta(\tau)^{\tfrac{k}{12}}f_{0}(\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt{1\over 2\pi i}{df_{0}\over d\tau}+h_{1}(\tau)f_{0}(\tau)=0

(8.2.4)

Furthermore, since
h_{1}
is a meromorphic function of weight 2 it may be expressed as
h_{1}(\tau)={{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)\over{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)}h_{0}(j)
, where
h_{0}
is a modular function of
\tau
which may be expressed as a function of
j(\tau)
.

Using the definition of
j(\tau)
in terms of
{\sf E}_{4}
and
{\sf E}_{6}
given by
j=12^{3}{\sf E}_{4}^{3}/({\sf E}_{4}^{3}-{\sf E}_{6}^{2})
, together with (
8.1.4
), we obtain the derivative of
j(\tau)
respect to
\tau
,

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}{dj\over d\tau}=-{{\sf E}_{6}\over{\sf E}_{4}}\,j

(8.2.5)

which by the chain rule also gives

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}{df_{0}\over d\tau}=-{{\sf E}_{6}\over{\sf E}_{4}}\,j\,{df_{0}(j)\over dj}

(8.2.6)

Cancelling a common factor of
{\sf E}_{6}/{\sf E}_{4}
, the equation for
f_{0}
now becomes,

\displaystyle j\,{d\ln f_{0}(j)\over dj}=h_{0}(j)

(8.2.7)

which may be solved by quadrature. It remains to ensure that the local solution
f(\tau)
obtained via this procedure is a proper modular form of weight
k
. This requirement may place conditions on the allowed values of
k
.

8.2.2

Second order MDE

Consider now the example of a second order MDE with holomorphic coefficient functions, acting on a weight-zero modular function. The most general such MDE takes the form,

\displaystyle[D_{2}D_{0}+\gamma{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)]f(\tau)=0

(8.2.8)

for some free constant parameter
\gamma
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle\left[\left({1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}\right)^{2}-{1\over 12\pi i}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau){d\over d\tau}+\gamma{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)\right]f(\tau)=0

(8.2.9)

The general solution to this equation may be obtained as follows. We begin by trading the derivatives with respect to
\tau
for derivatives with respect to the
j
-function, as we had done for the first order MDE, using (
8.2.5
). It follows that

\displaystyle{df\over dj}=-{{\sf E}_{4}\over j\,{\sf E}_{6}}{1\over 2\pi i}{df\over d\tau}

(8.2.10)

and by similar means,

\displaystyle{d^{2}f\over dj^{2}}=\left[\left({{\sf E}_{4}\over j\,{\sf E}_{6}}\right)^{2}\left(\left({1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}\right)^{2}-{{\sf E}_{2}\over 6}{1\over 2\pi i}{d\over d\tau}\right)-\left({7j-6912\over 6j(j-1728)}\right){d\over dj}\right]f

(8.2.11)

With these results the equation (
8.2.9
) can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle\left[j(j-1728){d^{2}\over dj^{2}}+{1\over 6}(7j-6912){d\over dj}+\gamma\right]f(\tau(j))=0

(8.2.12)

In terms of the variable
j(\tau)/1728
, this equation is of standard hypergeometric type. For generic choice of
\gamma
, there exist two distinct solutions given in closed form by,
18
18

18

When
\mu\in 6\,{\mathbb{N}}
, the second solution is divergent, since the third argument of the hypergeometric function is a negative integer. In this case, the second solution should be replaced by the Meijer G-function,

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)=G^{2,0}_{2,2}\left(\begin{matrix}{2\over 3}&1\\
{1-\mu\over 12}&{1+\mu\over 12}\end{matrix}\,\,\Big|1728\,j(\tau)^{-1}\right)

(8.2.13)

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j(\tau)^{-\tfrac{(1+\mu)}{12}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1+\mu}{12},\tfrac{5+\mu}{12}\,;\,1+\tfrac{\mu}{6}\,;\,1728\,j(\tau)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j(\tau)^{-{(1-\mu)\over 12}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1-\mu}{12},\tfrac{5-\mu}{12}\,;\,1-\tfrac{\mu}{6}\,;\,1728\,j(\tau)^{-1}\right)

(8.2.14)

where we have defined
\mu=\sqrt{1-144\gamma}
.

8.2.3

Third order MDE

We may similarly analyze the case of a third order modular differential equation with holomorphic coefficient functions and with
f
of weight 0. In this case the most general modular differential equation depends on two free parameters
a
and
b
,

\displaystyle\left[D_{4}D_{2}D_{0}+a{\sf E}_{4}(\tau)D_{0}+b{\sf E}_{6}(\tau)\right]f(\tau)=0

(8.2.15)

By switching to the local coordinate
j(\tau)
as before, we can again re-express this as a hypergeometric equation. For generic choices of parameters, there are three distinct solutions given by

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{-\tfrac{\mu_{1}+1}{6}}\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\tfrac{\mu_{1}+1}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{1}+3}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{1}+5}{6};\tfrac{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}{6}+1,\tfrac{\mu_{1}-\mu_{3}}{6}+1;1728\,j^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{-{\mu_{2}+1\over 6}}\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\tfrac{\mu_{2}+1}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{2}+3}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{2}+5}{6};\tfrac{\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}}{6}+1,\tfrac{\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}}{6}+1;1728\,j^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{-{\mu_{3}+1\over 6}}\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\tfrac{\mu_{3}+1}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{3}+3}{6},\tfrac{\mu_{3}+5}{6};\tfrac{\mu_{3}-\mu_{2}}{6}+1,\tfrac{\mu_{3}-\mu_{1}}{6}+1;1728\,j^{-1}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

(8.2.16)

Here we have defined
\mu_{i}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4x_{1}-2x_{2}-2x_{3}

\displaystyle\mu_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4x_{2}-2x_{3}-2x_{1}

\displaystyle\mu_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4x_{3}-2x_{1}-2x_{2}

(8.2.17)

and
x_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
as the three roots of the cubic equation

\displaystyle x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{2}+\left(a+\tfrac{1}{18}\right)x+b=0

(8.2.18)

8.2.4

Example

One interesting example is when the parameters in (
8.2.15
) are taken to be

\displaystyle a=-{107\over 2304}\hskip 57.81621ptb={23\over 55296}

(8.2.19)

In this case the exact solutions given above have the following
q
-expansions

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{23\over 48}\left(1+q+q^{2}+q^{3}+2q^{4}+2q^{5}+\dots\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-{1\over 48}}\left(1+q^{2}+q^{3}+2q^{4}+2q^{5}+\dots\right)

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{1\over 24}\left(1+q+q^{2}+2q^{3}+2q^{4}+3q^{5}+\dots\right)

(8.2.20)

To all order in
q
, these match with the following alternative closed-form expressions,

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\left(\sqrt{\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}-\sqrt{\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\left(\sqrt{\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}+\sqrt{\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}\right)

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}

(8.2.21)

Indeed, these can be checked to be the solutions to the MDE in question.

8.2.5

Modular invariance of solution spaces

Because MDEs are by definition modular invariant, so too are their solution spaces. This means that under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
the solutions must transform into linear combinations of themselves. It is useful to organize the solutions of an order-
d
MDE into a
d
-vector, in which case the
S
and
T
generators of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
can be represented by
d\times d
matrices. For example, in the concrete case of (
8.2.4
) we can organize the solutions into a three-dimensional vector
{\bf f}(\tau)=(f_{1}(\tau),\,f_{2}(\tau),\,f_{3}(\tau))^{t}
, and then from the known transformation properties of
\vartheta_{i}(0|\tau)
and
\eta(\tau)
we obtain
T
and
S
as

\displaystyle T=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{23\pi i\over 24}&0&0\\
0&e^{-{\pi i\over 24}}&0\\
0&0&e^{\pi i\over 12}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 36.135ptS={1\over 2}\left(\begin{matrix}1&\,1&-1\\
1&\,1&\,1\\
-2&\,2&\,0\end{matrix}\right)

(8.2.22)

This leads us to a fruitful generalization of the notion of modular forms, known as vector-valued modular forms.

8.3

Vector-valued modular forms

A
d
-dimensional vector-valued modular form
{\bf f}(\tau)=(f_{1}(\tau),\dots,f_{d}(\tau))^{t}
of weight
k
is defined by its modular transformation properties,

\displaystyle{\bf f}\left(\gamma\tau\right)=(c\tau+d)^{k}\rho(\gamma)\,{\bf f}(\tau)\hskip 36.135pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a\,\,&b\\
c\,\,&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(8.3.1)

where
\rho:SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow GL(d,{\mathbb{C}})
is a
d\times d
matrix representation.
{\bf f}(\tau)
is further required to be holomorphic in the upper-half plane and to have at most finite-order poles at the cusps.

8.3.1

Examples

We begin with some simple examples of vector-valued modular functions. We have already seen that solutions to an order-
d
MDE form a
d
-dimensional vector-valued modular form, and in the section
8.3.3
we will show that the converse is also true, i.e. the components of a
d
-dimensional vector-valued modular form are always solutions to an order-
d
MDE. In this subsection however we will momentarily ignore the connection to MDEs.

The Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions at
z=0
form a 3-dimensional vector-valued modular form
(\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|\tau),\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|\tau),\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|\tau))
. We have taken the eighth power to eliminate potential phases from the modular transformations given in (
3.8.7
). Under
S
and
T
transformations one has,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|\tau)\hskip 57.81621pt\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{4}\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|\tau)\hskip 57.81621pt\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|\tau)\hskip 57.81621pt\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{4}\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|\tau)

(8.3.2)

Clearly these act as permutations of the three elements. In the usual permutation notation, the
T
transformation corresponds to the transformation
(132)
, while the
S
transformation corresponds to
(321)
. Together, these generate the group
S_{3}
of permutations of three elements. One way of saying this is that there is a surjective homomorphism
\rho:SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow S_{3}
. It is interesting to ask what the kernel of the homomorphism
\rho
is. That is to say, we are interested in the group defined by,

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\rho)=\left\{\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\,\,\Big|\,\,\vartheta_{i}^{8}\left(0|\gamma\tau\right)=(c\tau+d)^{4}\vartheta_{i}^{8}(0|\tau),\quad i=2,3,4\right\}

(8.3.3)

Recalling the notation in terms of half-integer characteristics
\vartheta_{2}=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1/2\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\vartheta_{3}=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right],
and
\vartheta_{4}=\vartheta\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1/2\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, from (
3.8.7
) we see that elements of
{\rm Ker}(\rho)
must have,

\displaystyle a\alpha+c\beta\equiv\alpha\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,1)\hskip 57.81621ptb\alpha+d\beta\equiv\beta\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,1)

(8.3.4)

for any choice
(\alpha,\beta)\in\left\{(0,0),\left({1\over 2},0\right),\left(0,{1\over 2}\right)\right\}
. For
\alpha=\beta=0
the equations are trivially satisfied. Taking
\alpha={1\over 2}
and
\beta=0
, the two equations above require,

\displaystyle a\equiv 1\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2)\hskip 57.81621ptb\equiv 0\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2)

(8.3.5)

while taking
\alpha=0
and
\beta={1\over 2}
require,

\displaystyle c\equiv 0\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2)\hskip 57.81621ptd\equiv 1\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2)

(8.3.6)

We thus conclude that
{\rm Ker}(\rho)
is given by

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\rho)=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\,\,\Big|\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\equiv\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2)\right\}

(8.3.7)

In other words, we find that
{\rm Ker}(\rho)
is equal to the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(2)
.
Thus while the vector
(\vartheta_{2}^{8}(0|\tau),\vartheta_{3}^{8}(0|\tau),\vartheta_{4}^{8}(0|\tau))
transforms as a vector-valued modular form in a non-trivial permutation representation of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we see that each of the individual components of this vector is itself a genuine modular form under
\Gamma(2)
.

Proceeding to a four-dimensional example, we consider the vector
{\bf f}(\tau)
with components,

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3^{12}\,\eta(3\tau)^{24}\hskip 70.82428ptf_{2}(\tau)\,\,=\,\,\eta\left({\tau\over 3}\right)^{24}

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\left({\tau+1\over 3}\right)^{24}\hskip 57.81621ptf_{4}(\tau)\,\,=\,\,\eta\left({\tau+2\over 3}\right)^{24}

(8.3.8)

Under
S
and
T
modular transformations, we have the following

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)\hskip 57.81621ptf_{1}(-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{12}f_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)\hskip 57.81621ptf_{2}(-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{12}f_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{4}(\tau)\hskip 57.81621ptf_{3}(-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{12}f_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle f_{4}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{2}(\tau)\hskip 57.81621ptf_{4}(-1/\tau)\,\,=\,\,\tau^{12}f_{3}(\tau)

(8.3.9)

The generators
S
and
T
again permute elements of the vector; in permutation notation, they correspond to
(1342)
and
(2143)
, respectively. However, now these transformations do not generate the full group
S_{4}
of permutations of four elements. Instead, we only obtain the even permutations, i.e. we generate the alternating group
A_{4}
. So in this case we obtain a surjective homomorphism
\rho:SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow A_{4}
. We may again ask about the kernel of
\rho
. Demanding invariance of
f_{1}(\tau)
under
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
is equivalent to requiring,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}3&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}3&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{matrix}a&3b\\
c/3&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(8.3.10)

which in particular requires that
c\in 3{\mathbb{Z}}
. Similarly, demanding invariance of
f_{2}(\tau)
requires,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&3\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&3\end{matrix}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b/3\\
3c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(8.3.11)

which requires
b\in 3{\mathbb{Z}}
. Any permutation in
A_{4}
which fixes
f_{1}(\tau)
and
f_{2}(\tau)
also fixes
f_{3}(\tau)
and
f_{4}(\tau)
. We conclude that the kernel consists of all matrices for which
b\equiv c\equiv 0\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,3)
. This includes in particular the element
-I\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Hence the kernel is not quite
\Gamma(3)
, but rather
\pm\Gamma(3)
, where we use the shorthand
\pm\Gamma(N)=\Gamma(N)\times\{\pm{I}\}
. So we have found that while the vector
{\bf f}(\tau)
transforms as a vector-valued modular form in an alternating representation of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, each of the individual components of this vector is itself modular under the group
\pm\Gamma(3)
.

For completeness, let us record here some of the isomorphisms which we have seen, as well as some we have not yet seen,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\Gamma(2)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle S_{3}\hskip 57.81621ptSL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\pm\Gamma(3)\,\,\cong\,\,A_{4}

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\pm\Gamma(4)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle S_{4}\hskip 57.81621ptSL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})/\pm\Gamma(5)\,\,\cong\,\,A_{5}

(8.3.12)

These identities can be checked to be consistent with the index
[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}});\Gamma(N)]
, or equivalently with the order
|SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}_{N})|
, as given in (
6.2
) and (
6.3.9
). Indeed, from those formulas one finds

\displaystyle[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}});\Gamma(2)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6\hskip 57.81621pt[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}});\Gamma(3)]\,\,=\,\,24

\displaystyle\vphantom{,}[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}});\Gamma(4)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 48\hskip 57.81621pt[SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}});\Gamma(5)]\,\,=\,\,120

(8.3.13)

which can be compared with the orders of the relevant permutation and alternating groups,

\displaystyle|S_{3}|=6\hskip 36.135pt|A_{4}|=12\hskip 36.135pt|S_{4}|=24\hskip 36.135pt|A_{5}|=60

(8.3.14)

For
N=2
we see a match, whereas for
N>2
there is a factor of two difference, stemming from the fact that we are quotienting by
\pm\Gamma(N)
as opposed to
\Gamma(N)
.

8.3.2

Integrality and
\Gamma(N)

In both of the above examples, we found that the components of the vector-valued modular forms were themselves modular under
\Gamma(N)
for some appropriate
N
. This is not true in general, and it is interesting to ask when it holds. The following result has been proven for 2- and 3-dimensional vector-valued modular forms, and is conjectured to hold more generally:
19
19

19

In the context of vector-valued modular functions realized by the characters of RCFTs, which is the case of principal interest in Physics, this conjecture has been proven in
[
67
]
.

Conjecture 8.1
(Integrality Conjecture)

Consider a component
f_{i}(\tau)
of a vector-valued modular form with Fourier expansion

\displaystyle f_{i}(\tau)=q^{n^{(i)}_{0}}\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}^{(i)}q^{n}

(8.3.15)

with
n^{(i)}_{0}=p_{i}/N_{i}
and
(p_{i},N_{i})=1
. If all coefficients
a_{n}^{(i)}
are algebraic integers, then
f_{i}(\tau)
is a modular form for
\Gamma(N_{i})
.

The Integrality Conjecture states that a vector-valued modular form transforming in a representation
\rho
is integral (i.e. each component of the vector has integer Fourier coefficients) only if
\mathrm{Ker}(\rho)
contains a principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
. Indeed, the two examples given above had integer Fourier coefficients, and are directly in line with this conjecture. As another example, the three-dimensional vector-valued modular function given in (
8.2.4
) has all integer Fourier coefficients, and indeed the components can be seen to be modular functions for
\Gamma(48)
.

One common place in Physics where vector-valued modular forms appear is as characters of CFTs. Since the Fourier coefficients in that case are interpretable as physical degeneracies, they must of course be algebraic integers (and in fact rational integers). The conjecture then tells us that CFT characters are always modular functions for some
\Gamma(N)
. Going back to the example of (
8.2.4
), the functions in this case are actually characters for the Ising CFT,

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)=\chi_{\epsilon}(\tau)\hskip 36.135ptf_{2}(\tau)=\chi_{1}(\tau)\hskip 36.135ptf_{3}(\tau)=\chi_{\sigma}(\tau)

(8.3.16)

8.3.3

Relation to MDEs

We now show that the components of a
d
-dimensional vector-valued modular form are always solutions to an order-
d
MDE. To see this, we begin by constructing the Wronskians
W_{r}
associated to
{\bf f}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle W_{r}={\rm det\,}\left(\begin{matrix}f_{1}&\dots&f_{d}\\
{\cal D}^{(1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(1)}_{k}f_{d}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
{\cal D}^{(r-1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(r-1)}_{k}f_{d}\\
{\cal D}^{(r+1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(r+1)}_{k}f_{d}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
{\cal D}^{(d-1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(d-1)}_{k}f_{d}\\
{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{d}\end{matrix}\right)

(8.3.17)

for
r=1,\dots,d
. From the trivial identity
W_{d-1}-\left({W_{d-1}\over W_{d}}\right)W_{d}=0
, we obtain the following equation,

\displaystyle{\rm det\,}\left(\begin{matrix}f_{1}&\dots&f_{d}\\
{\cal D}^{(1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(1)}_{k}f_{d}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
{\cal D}^{(d-2)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(d-2)}_{k}f_{d}\\
{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{1}-{W_{d-1}\over W_{d}}{\cal D}^{(d-1)}_{k}f_{1}&\dots&{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{d}-{W_{d-1}\over W_{d}}{\cal D}^{(d-1)}_{k}f_{d}\end{matrix}\right)=0

(8.3.18)

For this determinant to vanish, the bottom row must be a linear combination of the other rows, which tells us that

\displaystyle{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{i}-{W_{d-1}\over W_{d}}{\cal D}^{(d-1)}_{k}f_{i}+\sum_{r=2}^{d}h_{r}(\tau){\cal D}^{(d-r)}_{k}f_{i}=0

(8.3.19)

This is of the usual MDE form (
8.2.1
), where in particular we have the coefficient function

\displaystyle h_{1}(\tau)=-{W_{d-1}\over W_{d}}

(8.3.20)

In fact, by plugging the expression for
{\cal D}^{(d)}_{k}f_{i}
obtained from (
8.3.19
) into (
8.3.17
), we may express all of the coefficient functions in terms of Wronskians, giving

\displaystyle h_{r}(\tau)=(-1)^{r}{W_{d-r}\over W_{d}}

(8.3.21)

Say that the function
f_{i}(\tau)
is bounded in the interior of the fundamental domain, and has zeroes at locations
p
of order
\mathrm{ord}_{f_{i}}(p)
. We define the so-called Wronskian index by

\displaystyle\ell:=6\left({1\over 2}\mathrm{ord}_{W_{d}}(i)+{1\over 3}\mathrm{ord}_{W_{d}}(\rho)+\sum_{p\in F}\mathrm{ord}_{W_{d}}(p)\right)

(8.3.22)

so that
\ell/6
is the number of zeroes of
W_{d}
. The Wronskian index can take any non-negative integer value except
1
. We note for future use that the valence formula (
3.6.1
) implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{ord}_{W_{d}}(i\infty)+{\ell\over 6}={d(k+d-1)\over 12}

(8.3.23)

where we have used the fact that the Wronskian has modular weight
d(k+d-1)
.

For
\ell=0
, the coefficient functions
h_{r}(\tau)
are holomorphic, which is the case analyzed in detail for
d=2,3
in the previous section. For
\ell>0
on the other hand, the coefficient functions will generically be singular. For example, consider the case for which
d=\ell=2
. By definition (
8.3.22
) we conclude that
\mathrm{ord}_{W_{2}}(\rho)=1
, and hence
W_{2}\sim E_{4}(\tau)
. The coefficient functions then take the form
h_{r}(\tau)\sim{1\over E_{4}(\tau)}W_{2-r}
. The remaining
\tau
dependence can be fixed by simply requiring that one has the correct modular weight, giving
h_{1}(\tau)\sim E_{4}(\tau)
and
h_{2}(\tau)\sim{E_{6}(\tau)\over E_{4}(\tau)}
.

Finding a solution to an MDE with meromorphic coefficients is often significantly more difficult than for its holomorphic counterpart (though for
d=2
and
\ell<6
it turns out that the solutions can again be written as hypergeometric functions). As we discuss in section
10.9
, Hecke operators can be used to generate such solutions.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

Solving modular differential equations by recasting them as hypergeometric equations has been discussed in
[
68
,
69
,
70
]
. Vector-valued modular forms grew out of work of Gunning
[
71
]
, as well as Selberg
[
72
]
, who used them as a way of studying Fourier coefficients of modular forms for non-congruence subgroups. For a modern review of various properties of vector-valued modular forms see
[
73
]
, and for a discussion of modular forms and differential operators see
[
74
]
. The integrality conjecture given in section
8.3.2
was first put forward in
[
75
]
, and has since been proven for two-dimensional and three-dimensional vector-valued modular forms in
[
76
,
77
]
and
[
78
,
79
]
, respectively. It has also been proven for vector-valued modular forms whose entries can be interpreted as the characters for RCFTs
[
67
]
. Its validity in a more general context has been discussed in e.g.
[
69
,
80
]
.

Finally, we note that modular differential equations have been applied to the classification of rational conformal field theories, starting with the seminal work in
[
81
,
82
]
, together with follow-up papers
[
83
,
84
]
shortly thereafter. In subsequent works modular differential techniques were applied to the classification of bosonic rational conformal fields theories with two characters at Wronskian index
\ell=2,4

[
85
,
86
,
87
]
, and to the classification of theories with three characters at
\ell=0

[
88
]
; for a recent review of some of this progress, see
[
89
]
.
As of the writing of these lecture notes, the state-of-the-art for the classification of bosonic rational conformal field theories is for theories with up to five characters (subject to restrictions on the Wronskian index), obtained in
[
90
]
. For fermionic theories on the other hand, there is a complicating factor that the relevant modular differential equations need not be invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but only an appropriate index 2 congruence subgroup. Works towards the classification of fermionic theories with small numbers of characters have appeared in
[
91
,
92
]
.

9

Modular graph functions and forms

Modular graph functions and forms map certain (decorated) graphs to complex-valued functions on the Poincaré upper half-plane
{\cal H}
with definite transformation properties under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The terminology of modular graph functions versus forms follows the terminology adopted for automorphic functions and forms in the holomorphic and non-holomorphic categories discussed in subsection
7.1.2
. Thus, modular graph functions may be viewed as
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant functions on
{\cal H}
, while modular graph forms may be identified with
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant differential forms.

To be more precise, we introduce the spaces
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
of complex-valued smooth functions
f:{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with the following
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformation properties,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{k,\ell}=\left\{f(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}(c\bar{\tau}+d)^{\ell}f(\tau)\hbox{ for all }\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\right\}

(9.0.1)

for
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{C}}
, subject to the condition
k-\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. This condition is imposed to ensure that the functions are single-valued. The functions of interest to us here will have at most polynomial growth at the cusp
\tau_{2}\to\infty
, and we shall impose this property also on the functions in
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
. A modular graph form of modular weight
(k,\ell)
is then a map from a certain (decorated) graph to
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
. For the special case
k=\ell
, one refers to such maps as modular graph functions. For
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we may equivalently view the functions in
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
as differential forms of weight
(\tfrac{k}{2},\tfrac{\ell}{2})
.

We shall establish that the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series introduced in section
4.2
may be associated with simple one-loop graphs and thus represent a special class of modular graph functions. Eisenstein series and modular graph functions and forms beyond Eisenstein series occur naturally and pervasively in the study of the low energy expansion of superstring amplitudes, as will be made clear in section
12
. Here we shall present a purely mathematical approach with only minimal reference to Physics.

9.1

One-loop modular graph functions are Eisenstein series

We begin by considering the scalar Green function
G(z-w|\tau)
on the torus
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
for a lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}+\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
with modulus
\tau\in{\cal H}
and
z,w\in\Sigma
. The scalar Green function was defined as the inverse of the scalar Laplacian on the torus on the space of functions orthogonal to constant functions in (
5.5.12
), solved in terms of Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions in (
5.5.14
), and shown to be invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
in (
5.5.19
). Here we shall use its expression in terms of a double Fourier series or Kronecker-Eisenstein series, given in (
5.5.8
) and expressed here in terms of a sum over the lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda\setminus\{0\}
,

\displaystyle G(z|\tau)=\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}{\tau_{2}\over\pi|m+n\tau|^{2}}\,e^{2\pi i(nx-my)}

(9.1.1)

where
z=x+\tau y
and
x,y
are real “co-moving coordinates” valued in
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
. By concatenating the Green function repeatedly using convolution, we may construct an infinite family of modular invariant functions, which may be defined recursively as follows,

\displaystyle G_{s}(z|\tau)=\int_{\Sigma}{d^{2}u\over\tau_{2}}\,G(z-u|\tau)G_{s-1}(u|\tau)

(9.1.2)

where we set
G_{1}=G
and
s
is an arbitrary positive integer. To the Green function
G
and its generalizations
G_{s}
for positive integer
s
it is natural to associate a graph, which physicists refer to as a Feynman graph,

G(z-w|\tau)~=~
z
w
G_{s}(z-w|\tau)~=~
z
u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
u_{s-1}
w

(9.1.3)

The black dots represent points
u_{1},\cdots u_{s-1}
that are integrated over
\Sigma
, while the white dots represent given points in
\Sigma
, that are not integrated. Upon Fourier transformation, convolution acts by multiplication, and we obtain the following representation for
G_{s}
,

\displaystyle G_{s}(z|\tau)=\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}{\tau_{2}^{s}\over\pi^{s}|m+n\tau|^{2s}}\,e^{2\pi i(nx-my)}

(9.1.4)

where
z=x+\tau y
. As long as
s\geq 2
, we may set the point
z=0
which graphically is equivalent to closing the open chain in (
9.1.3
) into the closed loop of (
9.1.5
),

E_{s}(\tau)~=~
u_{1}
u_{4}
u_{2}
u_{s}
u_{3}
u_{s-1}

(9.1.5)

Comparing to (
4.2.1
), we see that
G_{s}(0|\tau)=E_{s}(\tau)
, the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series. Actually, the double sum in (
9.1.4
) converges absolutely for any
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\rm Re\,}(s)>1
, but the graphical interpretation no longer makes sense when
s
is not a positive integer.

When
s
is integer and satisfies
s\geq 2
, the number
s
counts the number of edges in the graph (
9.1.5
) and is referred to as the weight of the graph (not to be confused with the modular weight of the corresponding modular graph function). In subsection
12.7
we shall identify this definition of the weight with that of transcendental weight.

9.2

Maass operators and Laplacians

Before studying higher-loop modular graph functions in detail, let us give a brief intermission on differential operators on the space
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
. There exist several natural maps between the spaces
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
which we shall now exhibit.

One noteworthy element of
{\cal M}_{-1,-1}
is the imaginary part
\tau_{2}
of the modulus
\tau
, whose powers satisfy
\tau_{2}^{s}\in{\cal M}_{-s,-s}
. Multiplication by a suitable power of
\tau_{2}
therefore provides a map between the different spaces
{\cal M}_{k,\ell}
. Since a function
f(\tau)
and its image
\tau_{2}^{s}f(\tau)
under this map are essentially equivalent to one another, we may introduce the equivalence relations,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{k,\ell}~\approx~{\cal M}_{k-\ell,0}~\approx~{\cal M}_{0,\ell-k}

(9.2.1)

This equivalence is particularly convenient when it comes to the action of partial differential operators. The Cauchy-Riemann operator
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
acts covariantly on the spaces
{\cal M}_{k,0}
without the need for a connection and maps to the space
{\cal M}_{k,2}
. Similarly, the operator
\partial_{\tau}
maps
{\cal M}_{0,\ell}
covariantly to the space
{\cal M}_{2,\ell}
without the need for a connection. One may, of course, include a
k
- and
\ell
-dependent connection to obtain the Maass operators (see appendix
C.4
). We shall circumvent doing so here by applying
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
to
{\cal M}_{k,0}
and
\partial_{\tau}
to
{\cal M}_{0,\ell}
only, using the equivalence of (
9.2.1
). Actually, we can multiply the Cauchy-Riemann operators by suitable powers of
\tau_{2}
so that the operators
\nabla=2i\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}
and
\bar{\nabla}=-2i\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
map the spaces as follows,

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}:{\cal M}_{k,0}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{k-2,0}

\displaystyle\nabla:{\cal M}_{0,\ell}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{0,\ell-2}

(9.2.2)

Laplace operators may be defined as follows,
20
20

20

Here we define the Laplacians as negative operators, while in appendix
C.4
they are positive operators.

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}^{+}:{\cal M}_{k,0}\to{\cal M}_{k,0}

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}^{+}=\tau_{2}^{-k}\,\nabla\,\tau_{2}^{k-2}\,\bar{\nabla}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ell}^{-}:{\cal M}_{0,\ell}\to{\cal M}_{0,\ell}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ell}^{-}=\tau_{2}^{-\ell}\,\bar{\nabla}\,\tau_{2}^{\ell-2}\,\nabla

(9.2.3)

On modular forms of weight
(0,0)
the Laplace operators
\Delta_{0}^{+}
and
\Delta_{0}^{-}
coincide and are denoted by
\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
. Famously, the Eisenstein series satisfies the Laplace eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle\Delta E_{s}=s(s-1)E_{s}

(9.2.4)

Its expansion at the cusp consists of two power behaved terms, plus an infinite number of exponentially suppressed terms, given in (
4.2.16
), and which we recall here for convenience,

\displaystyle E_{s}(\tau)=2\,{\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\,\tau_{2}^{s}+2{\Gamma(s-\tfrac{1}{2})\over\pi^{s-{1\over 2}}\Gamma(s)}\,\zeta(2s-1)\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}+{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}})

(9.2.5)

In terms of the independent variables
\tau,\bar{\tau}
and the real co-moving coordinates
x,y
, the Laplace operator
\Delta
also has
