G_{s}(x+\tau y|\tau)
as an eigenfunction with eigenvalue independent of
x,y
,

\displaystyle\Delta G_{s}(x+\tau y|\tau)=s(s-1)G_{s}(x+\tau y|\tau)

(9.2.6)

Application of the operator
\nabla^{k}
to
E_{s}
produces modular graph forms of weight
(0,-2k)
,

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}E_{s}(\tau)={\Gamma(s+k)\over\Gamma(s)}\sum_{(m,n)\not=(0,0)}{\tau_{2}^{s+k}\over\pi^{s}(m+n\tau)^{s+k}(m+n\bar{\tau})^{s-k}}

(9.2.7)

These functions coincide with Zagier’s elliptic polylogarithm, up to an overall normalization.

9.3

Two-loop modular graph functions

We now investigate the special class of modular graph functions in
{\cal M}_{0,0}
associated with arbitrary (decorated) two-loop graphs. A connected two-loop graph has two trivalent vertices and an arbitrary number of bivalent vertices, distributed on the three concatenated edges connecting the trivalent vertices at
z
and
w
according to three positive integers
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. The most general such graph is depicted in (
9.3.1
),

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)~=
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
z
w

(9.3.1)

The associated modular graph function may be defined in terms of a double integral over
\Sigma
of a triple product of concatenated Green functions
G_{s}
,

\displaystyle C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)=\int_{\Sigma}{d^{2}z\over\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma}{d^{2}w\over\tau_{2}}G_{a_{1}}(z-w|\tau)G_{a_{2}}(z-w|\tau)G_{a_{3}}(z-w|\tau)

(9.3.2)

Translation invariance on the torus renders one of the integrals redundant, and we have the following equivalent expression,

\displaystyle C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)=\int_{\Sigma}{d^{2}z\over\tau_{2}}G_{a_{1}}(z|\tau)G_{a_{2}}(z|\tau)G_{a_{3}}(z|\tau)

(9.3.3)

Upon Fourier transforming, the corresponding modular graph function is given by the following Kronecker-Eisenstein sums,

\displaystyle C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)=\sum_{{m_{r},n_{r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\atop r=1,2,3}}^{\prime}\delta\Big(\sum_{r=1}^{3}m_{r}\Big)\delta\Big(\sum_{r=1}^{3}n_{r}\Big)\prod_{r=1}^{3}\left({\tau_{2}\over\pi|m_{r}+n_{r}\tau|^{2}}\right)^{a_{r}}

(9.3.4)

The sums are absolutely convergent provided
a_{r}+a_{s}>1
for all pairs
r\not=s
. The weight is given by
w=a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}
and is required to satisfy
w\geq 3
for convergence. The Kronecker-Eisenstein sums are absolutely convergent for complex values of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
as long as
{\rm Re\,}(a_{1}),{\rm Re\,}(a_{2}),{\rm Re\,}(a_{3})>1
, but contrarily to the case of the Eisenstein series, the problem of the existence of analytic continuations in these variables remains unexplored.

Two key properties of the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series, namely that they satisfy a differential equation, and have a simple expansion at the cusp, have more complicated counterparts in two-loop modular graph functions.

Theorem 9.1

Two-loop modular graph functions
C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}
of weight
w=a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}
obey a system of differential equations of uniform weight
w

\displaystyle 2\Delta C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2a_{1}a_{2}\,C_{a_{1}+1,a_{2}-1,a_{3}}+a_{1}a_{2}\,C_{a_{1}+1,a_{2}+1,a_{3}-2}-4a_{1}a_{2}\,C_{a_{1}+1,a_{2},a_{3}-1}

\displaystyle+a_{1}(a_{1}-1)\,C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}+\hbox{ 5 permutations of }(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})

where
\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
. When
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the system of differential equations
for a given weight truncates to a finite-dimensional linear system of inhomogeneous equations where the inhomogeneous part consists of a linear combination of
E_{w}
and
E_{w_{1}}E_{w_{2}}
with
w=w_{1}+w_{2}
and
w_{1},w_{2}\geq 2
, as a consequence of the following relations,

\displaystyle C_{w_{1},w_{2},0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{w_{1}}E_{w_{2}}-E_{w}

\displaystyle C_{w_{1}+1,w_{2},-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{w_{1}}E_{w_{2}}+E_{w_{1}+1}E_{w_{2}-1}

(9.3.5)

The eigenvalues of the linear system are of the form
s(s-1)
where
1\leq s\leq w-2
and
w-s\in 2{\mathbb{N}}
and have multiplicity
[(s+2)/3]
.

For example, up to weight 5, the relations of Theorem
9.1
are given as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta C_{1,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6E_{3}

\displaystyle(\Delta-2)C_{2,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 9E_{4}-E_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\Delta-6)C_{3,1,1}-3C_{2,2,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16E_{5}-4E_{2}E_{3}

\displaystyle\Delta C_{2,2,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8E_{5}

(9.3.6)

Note that the first and last identities imply
C_{1,1,1}-E_{3}
is constant, as must be
5C_{2,2,1}-2E_{5}
. These constants may be determined from the asymptotic behavior of these functions at the cusp, a problem to which we now turn.

Theorem 9.2

The expansion of
C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)
near the cusp
\tau\to i\infty
is given by a Laurent polynomial in
\tau_{2}
of degree
(w,1-w)

\displaystyle C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}(\tau)=c_{w}(-4\pi\tau_{2})^{w}+{c_{2-w}\over(4\pi\tau_{2})^{w-2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{w-1}{c_{w-2k-1}\,\zeta(2k+1)\over(4\pi\tau_{2})^{2k+1-w}}+{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}})

(9.3.7)

where
c_{w},c_{w-2k-1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
c_{2-w}
is a linear combination with integer coefficients of products of odd zeta-values,

\displaystyle c_{2-w}=\sum_{k=1}^{w-2}\gamma_{k}\,\zeta(2k+1)\zeta(2w-2k-3)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(9.3.8)

A consequence of this theorem is that no multiple zeta-values occur in the Laurent polynomial of two-loop modular graph functions. It was shown by Zerbini that higher loop modular graph functions do involve irreducible multiple zeta values.

At every odd weight, there is one linear combination of the above differential identities in the subspace of vanishing eigenvalue whose inhomogeneous part does not involve bilinears in the Eisenstein series. This property may be verified in the identities for
C_{1,1,1}
and
C_{2,2,1}
in (
9.1
), and holds for all odd weights. Each one of these identities may be integrated up to an additive constant which may be fixed by the asymptotics near the cusp, and we find,

\displaystyle C_{1,1,1}

\displaystyle=E_{3}+\zeta(3)

\displaystyle C_{3,3,1}+C_{3,2,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{7}E_{7}+\frac{\zeta(7)}{252}

\displaystyle C_{2,2,1}

\displaystyle={2\over 5}E_{5}+{\zeta(5)\over 30}

\displaystyle 9C_{4,4,1}+18C_{4,3,2}+4C_{3,3,3}

\displaystyle=4E_{7}+\frac{\zeta(9)}{240}

(9.3.9)

The equation for
C_{1,1,1}
was derived by Zagier in an unpublished note by direct summation of the Kronecker-Eisenstein series which defines
C_{1,1,1}
.

9.4

General modular graph functions and forms

At higher loops, it is no longer true that the Laplace operator in
\tau
, (acting on the immediate generalization of two-loop modular graph functions to higher loops) closes onto functions defined by Kronecker-Eisenstein series in which the exponents of holomorphic momenta
m_{r}+n_{r}\tau
and their complex conjugate coincide. It is natural to also lift the restriction to modular functions, and consider more generally modular graph forms which have non-trivial modular weights. The required generalization may be defined as follows.

The connectivity matrix
\Gamma
of an arbitrary graph has components
\Gamma_{v\,r}
where the index
v=1,\cdots,V
labels the vertices of the graph and the index
r=1,\cdots,R
labels its edges. No edge is allowed to begin and end on the same vertex. When edge
r
contains vertex
v
we have
\Gamma_{v\,r}=\pm 1
, while otherwise we have
\Gamma_{v\,r}=0
. We shall view the pair of exponents
(a_{r},b_{r})
of the momenta
p_{r}=m_{r}+n_{r}\tau
and its complex conjugate
\bar{p}_{r}
through edge
r
as providing a decoration of the graph. The decorated graph is then specified by the connectivity matrix
\Gamma
and the pair of arrays
(A,B)
defined by,

\displaystyle A=[a_{1},\cdots,,a_{R}]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{R}

\displaystyle B=[b_{1},\cdots,,b_{R}]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}=b_{1}+\cdots+b_{R}

(9.4.1)

where
a_{r},b_{r}\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
a_{r}-b_{r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for all
r=1,\cdots,R
. The pair of total exponents
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})
generalizes the weight to the full decorated graph.

To a decorated graph
(\Gamma,A,B)
we associate a complex-valued function on
{\cal H}
, defined by the following Kronecker-Eisenstein sum, whenever this sum is absolutely convergent,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right](\tau)=\left({\tau_{2}\over\pi}\right)^{{1\over 2}\mathfrak{a}+{1\over 2}\mathfrak{b}}\sum_{p_{1},\dots,p_{R}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}~\prod_{r=1}^{R}{1\over(p_{r})^{a_{r}}~(\bar{p}_{r})^{b_{r}}}\,\prod_{v=1}^{V}\delta\left(\sum_{s=1}^{R}\Gamma_{v\,s}\,p_{s}\right)

(9.4.2)

The Kronecker
\delta
-symbol equals 1 when its argument vanishes as an element of the lattice
\Lambda
, and equals 0 otherwise. The number of loops
L
is the number of independent momenta, given by
L=R-V+1
. For any given decorated graph
(\Gamma,A,B)
, the domain of absolute convergence of the sums in (
9.4.2
) is given by a system of inequalities on the combinations
{\rm Re\,}(a_{r}+b_{r})
. Outside of the domain of convergence, it is an open question as to whether and how the functions
{\cal C}
of (
9.4.2
) may be defined by analytic continuation in the variables
a_{r}+b_{r}
.
The function
{\cal C}
in (
9.4.2
) vanishes whenever the integer
\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}
is odd, or whenever it is associated with a graph
\Gamma
which becomes disconnected upon severing a single edge. A function
{\cal C}
associated with a graph
\Gamma
which is the union of two graphs
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, such that the intersection of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
consists of a single vertex, factorizes into the product of functions
{\cal C}
for
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, with the corresponding partitions of the exponents.

9.4.1

Modular properties

Under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
the functions defined in (
9.4.2
) transform as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right]\left({a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\right)=\left({c\tau+d\over c\bar{\tau}+d}\right)^{{1\over 2}\mathfrak{a}-{1\over 2}\mathfrak{b}}{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right](\tau)

(9.4.3)

where
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
ad-bc=1
. One defines the modular weight of
{\cal C}
by the pair
({1\over 2}(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}),{1\over 2}(\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}))
which is integer-valued for
\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}
even (
{\cal C}
vanishes for odd
\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}
). As we have explained above, we refer to
{\cal C}
as a modular graph form when
\mathfrak{a}\not=\mathfrak{b}
and a modular graph function when
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{b}
.
For
\mathfrak{a}\not=\mathfrak{b}
the normalization factor of
\tau_{2}
is not canonical, and we introduce the forms
{\cal C}^{+}
and
{\cal C}^{-}
of modular weight
(0,\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a})
and
(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b},0)
respectively as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\Gamma}^{\pm}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right]=(\tau_{2})^{\pm{\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}\over 2}}{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\end{matrix}\right](\tau)

(9.4.4)

{\cal C}^{+}_{\Gamma}
transforms with a factor
(c\bar{\tau}+d)^{-\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{b}}
while
{\cal C}^{-}_{\Gamma}
transforms with a factor
(c\tau+d)^{\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}}
.

9.4.2

Examples: Eisenstein series and dihedral graphs

In this newfound notation, we may represent and generalize some of the examples of modular graph functions and forms encountered earlier. The Eisenstein series and its successive derivatives take the form,

\displaystyle E_{s}={\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}s&0\cr s&0\end{matrix}\right]\hskip 57.81621pt\nabla^{k}E_{s}={\Gamma(s+k)\over\Gamma(s)}\,{\cal C}^{+}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}s+k&0\cr s-k&0\end{matrix}\right]

(9.4.5)

where
\Gamma
is a connected one-loop graph with only bivalent vertices. The two-loop modular graph functions
C_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}
may be generalized to
(n-1)
-loop modular graph functions,

\displaystyle C_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}={\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1}&\cdots&a_{n}\cr a_{1}&\cdots&a_{n}\end{matrix}\right]

(9.4.6)

where the “dihedral” graph
\Gamma
contains two vertices of valence
n+1
in addition to the remaining vertices which are all bivalent. For
n=3
we recover the two-loop modular graph functions studied earlier.

9.4.3

Examples: holomorphic modular graph forms

When all anti-holomorphic exponents
b_{i}
vanish identically the corresponding modular graph form reduces to the following expression,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\Gamma}^{-}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1}&\cdots&a_{n}\cr 0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right](\tau)=\sum_{p_{1},\dots,p_{R}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}~\prod_{r=1}^{R}{1\over(p_{r})^{a_{r}}}\,\prod_{v=1}^{V}\delta\left(\sum_{s=1}^{R}\Gamma_{v\,s}\,p_{s}\right)

(9.4.7)

The multiple sum is absolutely convergent provided
a_{r}\geq 2
for all
r=1,\cdots,R\geq 2
. In this case the result is
holomorphic in
\tau
and a modular form of weight
(\mathfrak{a},0)
with
\mathfrak{a}=a_{1}+\cdots,+a_{R}
. When
{\cal C}^{-}_{\Gamma}
is a modular form of modular weight
(\mathfrak{a},0)
, it may be expressed as a polynomial in the holomorphic modular forms
{\sf E}_{4}
and
{\sf E}_{6}
, and therefore produces a map from graphs
\Gamma
to holomorphic modular forms, or equivalently to polynomials in
{\sf E}_{4}
and
{\sf E}_{6}
. The nature of this map, which is much simpler than the map to non-holomorphic modular forms, remains to be investigated. Under weaker conditions of conditional convergence the sum must be suitably regularized. Maintaining holomorphicity leads to a violation of full modular invariance, such as is the case with
a_{1}=2,a_{2}=0
for
R=2
which yields the quasi-modular form
{\sf E}_{2}
.

9.4.4

Algebraic relations

Under complex conjugation, any of these functions have their arrays of holomorphic and anti-holomorphic exponents swapped,

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right](\tau)^{*}={\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}B\cr A\cr\end{matrix}\right](\tau)

(9.4.8)

In addition, modular graph forms obey momentum conservation identities,

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{R}\Gamma_{k\,r}\,{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A-S_{r}\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right]=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\Gamma_{k\,r}\,{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B-S_{r}\cr\end{matrix}\right]=0

(9.4.9)

where the
R
-dimensional row-vector
S_{r}
is defined to have zeroes in all slots except for the
r
-th, which instead has value 1,

\displaystyle S_{r}=[\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{r-1},1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{R-r}]

(9.4.10)

These identities simply express the fact that the lattice momenta entering each vertex
v
add up to zero or, as physicists would state, that momenta is conserved at each vertex as a consequence of translation invariance on the torus.

9.4.5

Differential relations

The action of the Cauchy-Riemann operator
\nabla=2i\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}
on modular graph forms
{\cal C}^{+}
defined and normalized in (
9.4.4
) for arbitrary exponents
A,B
is given by,

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal C}_{\Gamma}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right]=\sum_{r=1}^{R}a_{r}\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}A+S_{r}\cr B-S_{r}\end{matrix}\right]

(9.4.11)

where
S_{r}
was defined in (
9.4.10
). The action of the Laplace operator
\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\partial_{\tau}
on modular graph functions (for which
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{b}
) is given by,

\displaystyle(\Delta+\mathfrak{a})\,{\cal C}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}A\cr B\cr\end{matrix}\right]=\sum_{r,s=1}^{R}a_{r}b_{s}\,{\cal C}\left[\begin{matrix}A+S_{r}-S_{s}\cr B-S_{r}+S_{s}\end{matrix}\right]

(9.4.12)

It is similarly possible to define the action of the Laplace operator on modular graph forms of arbitrary modular weight with
\mathfrak{a}\not=\mathfrak{b}
, but we shall not need them here.

9.4.6

Higher loop algebraic identities

Higher-loop modular graph functions and forms satisfy systems of algebraic and differential identities which generalize those found for two-loops. For example, one has the following algebraic identities involving modular graph functions of weight 4 and 5,

\displaystyle C_{1,1,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 24C_{2,1,1}+3E_{2}^{2}-18E_{4}

\displaystyle C_{1,1,1,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 60C_{3,1,1}+10E_{2}\,C_{1,1,1}-48E_{5}+16\zeta(5)

\displaystyle 40C_{2,1,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 300C_{3,1,1}+120E_{2}E_{3}-276E_{5}+7\zeta(5)

(9.4.13)

These identities were proven using holomorphic subgraph reduction and a sieve algorithm. We shall discuss the techniques used in the proof of these identities in the next subsection for the example of the first identity. We refer to the bibliographical notes for the papers where all algebraic identities between modular graphs of weight up to six, and some selected identities of weight 7 are proven, and where a Mathematica package is now available.

9.5

Relating modular graph functions to holomorphic forms

The Maass operator
\nabla=2i\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}
maps a modular graph form of modular weight
(0,\ell)
to a modular graph form of modular weight
(0,\ell-2)
, as was shown in subsection
9.2
. When the derivative is applied to a non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{n}
, for example, and repeated
n
times, one obtains the following form, using (
9.4.5
),

\displaystyle\nabla^{n}E_{n}={\Gamma(2n)\over\Gamma(n)}\,{\cal C}^{+}_{\Gamma}\left[\begin{matrix}2n~0\cr 0~~\,0\end{matrix}\right]={\Gamma(2n)\over\Gamma(n)\pi^{n}}\,\tau_{2}^{2n}\,G_{2n}(\tau)

(9.5.1)

where
G_{2n}(\tau)
is the holomorphic Eisenstein series of modular weight
(2n,0)
.

Applying the Maass operator to more complicated modular graph functions is not as simple as in the case of non-holomorphic Eisenstein series, but nonetheless a similar pattern emerges:
\nabla
decreases the anti-holomorphic exponents and increases the holomorphic exponents. Let’s see how this works in the case of the modular graph function
C_{1,1,1,1}
. Using the rules of (
9.4.11
) for
\nabla
, we obtain for the first derivative,

\displaystyle\nabla C_{1,1,1,1}=\nabla{\cal C}\left[\begin{matrix}1~1~1~1\cr 1~1~1~1\end{matrix}\right]=4\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}2~1~1~1\cr 0~1~1~1\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.2)

The form on the right has modular weight
(0,-2)
. Applying
\nabla
again gives,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}C_{1,1,1,1}=12\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}2~2~1~1\cr 0~0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]-24\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}3~1~1~1\cr 0~0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.3)

where to obtain the last term we have a momentum conservation identity to convert a negative entry on the lower row,

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}3&1~1~1\cr-1&1~1~1\end{matrix}\right]=-3\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}3~1~1~1\cr 0~0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.4)

Further application of
\nabla
to (
9.5.3
), in its present form, would yield modular graph functions with some negative anti-holomorphic exponents that cannot be removed simply by using momentum conservation identities. However, the subgraph corresponding to the first two entries forms a closed loop with only holomorphic momenta.
The momentum summations in such holomorphic subgraphs may be evaluated explicitly in terms of holomorphic modular forms. This procedure is dubbed holomorphic subgraph reduction and, in the present case, leads to the following identity,

\displaystyle{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}2~2~1~1\cr 0~0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]-2\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}3~1~1~1\cr 0~0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]=2\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}4~1~1\cr 0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]+3\,{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}4~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]{\cal C}^{+}\left[\begin{matrix}2~0\cr 2~0\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.5)

Collecting all contributions to
\nabla^{2}C_{1,1,1,1}
and proceeding analogously for the entries
C_{2,1,1}
and
E_{2}^{2}
in the first identity in (
9.4.6
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}C_{1,1,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 24\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~1~1\cr 0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]+36\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}2~0\cr 2~0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}C_{2,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~1~1\cr 0~1~1\end{matrix}\right]+6\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}3~2~1\cr 1~0~1\end{matrix}\right]+4\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}6~0\cr 2~0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}E_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}3~0\cr 1~0\end{matrix}\right]^{2}+12\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}2~0\cr 2~0\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.6)

Applying
\nabla
to either
\nabla^{2}C_{1,1,1,1}
or to
E_{2}^{2}
produces modular graph functions with negative entries in the lower row that cannot be removed by using momentum conservation identities. This occurs when
\nabla
is applied to one of the modular graph functions that is proportional to a holomorphic modular form, such as the first factors in the last terms on the right of
\nabla^{2}C_{1,1,1,1}
and
\nabla^{2}E_{2}^{2}
. However,
C_{2,1,1}
and the linear combination
C_{1,1,1,1}-3E_{2}^{2}
(that cancels the last terms discussed above) produces the following
\nabla^{3}
derivatives,

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}(C_{1,1,1,1}-3E_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 432\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}7~0\cr 1~0\end{matrix}\right]-288\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}3~0\cr 1~0\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}C_{2,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 108\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}7~0\cr 1~0\end{matrix}\right]-12\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}4~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}3~0\cr 1~0\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.7)

Proceeding analogously for the fourth
\nabla
-derivative, we obtain,

\displaystyle\nabla^{4}(C_{1,1,1,1}-24C_{2,1,1}-3E_{2}^{2})=-15120\,{\cal C}^{+}\!\left[\begin{matrix}8~0\cr 0~0\end{matrix}\right]

(9.5.8)

The right side is proportional to
\tau_{2}^{8}G_{8}
, where
G_{8}
is the holomorphic Eisenstein series of weight
(8,0)
.
But this form is proportional to
\nabla^{4}E_{4}
, so that we obtain,

\displaystyle\nabla^{4}\big(C_{1,1,1,1}-24C_{2,1,1}-3E_{2}^{2}+18E_{4}\big)=0

(9.5.9)

Next, we invoke the following lemma.

Lemma 9.3

Let
F
be a non-holomorphic modular function with polynomial growth near the cusp as
\tau_{2}\to\infty
. If
F
satisfies the differential equation,

\displaystyle\nabla^{n}F=0

(9.5.10)

for an arbitrary integer
n\geq 1
, then
F
is constant as a function of
\tau
.

A simple proof of the lemma proceeds by making use of the following formula,

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}^{n}(\tau_{2})^{-2n}\nabla^{n}=\prod_{s=1}^{n}\Big(\Delta-s(s-1)\Big)

(9.5.11)

This formula immediately implies that
F
must satisfy the weaker condition,

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{n}\Big(\Delta-s(s-1)\Big)F=0

(9.5.12)

Being a modular graph function,
F
has at most polynomial growth at the cusp. The solution to the equation
\Delta f_{s}=s(s-1)f_{s}
for positive integer
s
is either a constant for
s=1
or an Eisenstein series
E_{s}
for
s\geq 2
. This implies that
F
must be a linear combination of a constant and the Eisenstein series
E_{s}
for
s=2,\cdots,n
. Returning now to the original equation (
9.5.10
) in the formulation of the lemma and using the asymptotics of the Eisenstein series at the cusp of (
4.2.16
), we conclude that the coefficients of the Eisenstein series in
F
must all vanish so that
F
is constant. This completes the proof of the lemma.

Applying the lemma to (
9.5.9
), it follows that,

\displaystyle F_{4}=C_{1,1,1,1}-24C_{2,1,1}-3E_{2}^{2}+18E_{4}

(9.5.13)

must be constant. To calculate the constant, we investigate the asymptotics at the cusp.
Clearly, all
\tau
-dependent terms must cancel on the right side as one may verify using the expansions of
C_{1,1,1,1}
and
C_{2,1,1}
in terms of the variable
y=\pi\tau_{2}
,

\displaystyle C_{1,1,1,1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{y^{4}\over 945}+{2\zeta(3)y\over 3}+{10\zeta(5)\over y}-{3\zeta(3)^{2}\over y^{2}}+{9\zeta(7)\over 4y^{3}}+{\cal O}(e^{-2y})

\displaystyle C_{2,1,1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{2y^{4}\over 14175}+{\zeta(3)y\over 45}+{5\zeta(5)\over 12y}-{\zeta(3)^{2}\over 4y^{2}}+{9\zeta(7)\over 16y^{3}}+{\cal O}(e^{-2y})

(9.5.14)

and the expansion of the Eisenstein series in (
4.2.16
). The constant
y^{0}
term has vanishing coefficient in each of the the modular graph functions in
F_{4}
, so that
F_{4}=0
. This completes the proof of the first identity in (
9.4.6
). The other identities in (
9.4.6
) may be established with the same methods, and holomorphic subgraph reduction formulas that generalize (
9.5.5
).

9.6

Modular graph functions and iterated modular integrals

The Fourier expansion of non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{k}(\tau)
for integer
k\geq 2
, presented in (
4.2.17
), shows that the Fourier non-zero modes are of the form
(q^{N}+\bar{q}^{N})
times a polynomial in
1/\tau_{2}
.
Such a simple form is no longer shared by modular graph functions and forms at higher loops since in that case terms of the form
q^{M}\bar{q}^{N}
also occur. Nonetheless, modular graph forms are related to holomorphic modular forms, even at higher loop, as demonstrated by the fact that certain repeated derivatives
\nabla
map to holomorphic modular forms. Another way to exhibit this connection concretely is via iterated modular integrals.

The basic building blocks of iterated modular integrals are the functions
{\cal E}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)
and
{\cal E}^{0}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)
defined iteratively by the following relations,

\displaystyle{\cal E}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi i\int^{i\infty}_{\tau}{d\tau_{r}\over(2\pi i)^{k_{r}}}\,G_{k_{r}}(\tau_{r})\,{\cal E}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r-1}|\tau)

\displaystyle{\cal E}^{0}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi i\int^{i\infty}_{\tau}{d\tau_{r}\over(2\pi i)^{k_{r}}}\,G^{0}_{k_{r}}(\tau_{r})\,{\cal E}^{0}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r-1}|\tau)

(9.6.1)

The holomorphic modular form
G_{k}(\tau)
of weight
k
is defined for
k\geq 3
by the by now familiar absolutely convergent Kronecker-Eisenstein series,

\displaystyle G_{k}(\tau)=\sum_{{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\atop(m,n)\not=(0,0)}}{1\over(m+n\tau)^{k}}=2\zeta(k)+G^{0}_{k}(\tau)

(9.6.2)

The function
G_{k}(\tau)
vanishes for all odd
k\geq 3
and evaluates to
2\zeta(k)
at the cusp, while
G^{0}_{k}(\tau)
consists of the non-constant Fourier modes of
G_{k}(\tau)
, and decays exponentially at the cusp. We extend the definition to
k=0
and supply initial conditions for the iterated integrals,

\displaystyle G_{0}(\tau)=~G^{0}_{0}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1

\displaystyle{\cal E}(\cdot|\tau)={\cal E}^{0}(\cdot|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

(9.6.3)

In view of the exponential decay of
G^{0}_{k}(\tau)
at the cusp for
k\geq 4
, the corresponding integrals are convergent, while all other integrations are defined via the tangential base-point prescription at the cusp, which essentially amounts to the following prescription,

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{i\infty}d\tau_{r}\to-\tau

(9.6.4)

The iterated integrals satsify the following differential equations,

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal E}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{4\pi\tau_{2}^{2}\over(2\pi i)^{k_{r}}}\,G_{k_{r}}(\tau)\,{\cal E}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r-1}|\tau)

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal E}^{0}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{4\pi\tau_{2}^{2}\over(2\pi i)^{k_{r}}}\,G^{0}_{k_{r}}(\tau)\,{\cal E}^{0}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r-1}|\tau)

(9.6.5)

For example, we have,

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal E}^{0}(2k|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{4\pi\tau_{2}^{2}\over(2\pi i)^{2k}}\,G^{0}_{2k}(\tau)

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal E}^{0}(2k,0_{n}|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi\tau_{2}^{2}\,{\cal E}^{0}(2k,0_{n-1}|\tau)

(9.6.6)

where the notation
0_{n}
stands for an array of
n
zeros.

We begin by showing how the simplest Eisenstein series
E_{2}
may be expressed in terms of these iterated integrals. The starting point is the differential equation obtained in (
9.5.1
),

\displaystyle\pi^{2}\nabla^{2}E_{2}=6\tau_{2}^{4}\,G_{4}={2\pi^{4}\tau_{2}^{4}\over 15}+6\tau_{2}^{4}\,G_{4}^{0}

(9.6.7)

Using the relation
\nabla\tau_{2}^{n}=n\tau_{2}^{n+1}
and eliminating
G_{4}^{0}
in favor of
\nabla{\cal E}^{0}(4|\tau)
using the first equation in (
9.6
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\pi^{2}\nabla^{2}E_{2}={2\pi^{4}\over 45}\nabla\tau_{3}^{3}+24\pi^{3}\tau_{2}^{2}\,\nabla{\cal E}^{0}(4|\tau)

(9.6.8)

Successive use of the second relation in (
9.6
) for
n=1,2
allows us to recast the right side in the form of a total
derivative in terms of the iterated integrals
{\cal E}^{0}(4|\tau),{\cal E}^{0}(4,0|\tau),{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)
,

\displaystyle\nabla(\pi\nabla E_{2})=\nabla\Big({2\pi^{3}\tau_{2}^{3}\over 45}+24\pi^{2}\tau_{2}^{2}{\cal E}^{0}(4|\tau)+12\pi\tau_{2}{\cal E}^{0}(4,0|\tau)+3{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)\Big)

(9.6.9)

The equation is readily solved, and the general solution is given as follows,

\displaystyle\pi\nabla E_{2}={2\pi^{3}\tau_{2}^{3}\over 45}+24\pi^{2}\tau_{2}^{2}\,{\cal E}^{0}(4|\tau)+12\pi\tau_{2}\,{\cal E}^{0}(4,0|\tau)+3\,{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)+\overline{f_{1}(\tau)}

(9.6.10)

where
f_{1}(\tau)
is holomorphic. Making use anew of the equations (
9.6
) we can integrate this equation once more, and find,

\displaystyle E_{2}(\tau)={\pi^{2}\tau_{2}^{2}\over 45}-6\,{\cal E}^{0}(4,0|\tau)-{3\over\pi\tau_{2}}{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)-{\overline{f_{1}(\tau)}\over\pi\tau_{2}}+\overline{f_{2}(\tau)}

(9.6.11)

where
f_{2}(\tau)
is another holomorphic function. The reality of
E_{2}
determines
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
uniquely up to an additive constant in
f_{1}
, which may be fixed by the known asymptotics at the cusp. Using again the shorthand
y=\pi\tau_{2}
, we have,

\displaystyle E_{2}(\tau)={y^{2}\over 45}+{\zeta(3)\over y}-12\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0|\tau)\big]-{6\over y}{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)\big]

(9.6.12)

The above expression reflects the structure of the Fourier decomposition of the Eisenstein series mentioned at the beginning of this section.

Next, we provide an example of the decomposition into iterated integrals for the simplest modular graph function that is not an Eisenstein series, namely
C_{2,1,1}
. The differential equations for
C_{2,1,1}
, given in the second line of (
9.5
), may be recast in the following form,

\displaystyle\pi^{2}\nabla^{3}\big(C_{2,1,1}-\tfrac{9}{10}E_{4}\big)=-6\tau_{2}^{2}G_{4}(\tau)\nabla E_{2}

(9.6.13)

The subtraction of the term in
E_{4}
is convenient as it readily cancels the first term on the right side of the second line in (
9.5
). One may begin by using expression given in (
9.6.10
) to eliminate
\nabla E_{2}
from the above equation in favor of iterated integrals. Successive use of (
9.6
) again allows us to proceed by the same methods as used for
E_{2}
, and one obtains,

\displaystyle C_{2,1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{y^{4}}{20250}+\tfrac{\zeta(3)y}{45}+\tfrac{5\zeta(5)}{12y}-\tfrac{\zeta(3)^{2}}{4y^{2}}-\left(\tfrac{2y}{15}-\tfrac{3\zeta(3)}{y^{2}}\right){\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)\big]

(9.6.14)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{9}{y^{2}}\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{2}|\tau)\big]^{2}-72\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4_{2},0_{2}|\tau)\big]-\tfrac{1}{5}\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{3}|\tau)\big]

\displaystyle-\tfrac{36}{y}\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0,4,0_{2}|\tau)\big]-\tfrac{108}{y}\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4_{2},0_{3}|\tau)\big]-\tfrac{1}{10y}\,{\rm Re\,}\big[{\cal E}^{0}(4,0_{4}|\tau)\big]

In this formulation, the first term on the second line reveals the presence of terms of the form
q^{M}\bar{q}^{N}
in the Fourier series of
C_{2,1,1}
, as expected.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The study of modular graph functions originated with explorations into the low energy effective interactions produced by one-loop superstring amplitudes
[
93
]
, as will be described in sections
12
and
14
. Systematic investigations of modular graph functions were initiated in
[

,

,

]
. The proof of Theorem

was given in
[

, while the proof of Theorem

was given in
[

, where explicit expressions for all the coefficients in the expansion were also provided. The Poincaré series for
\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and Fourier series were computed in
[
98
]
. The relation between modular graph functions and single-valued elliptic polylogarithms was exhibited in
[
99
]
. Introductions to elliptic polylogarithms may be found in
[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
.

The systematic construction of the algebraic and differential identities satisfied by modular graph functions and forms was carried out in
[
104
,
105
,
106
]

using the method of holomorphic subgraph reduction. A variety of other methods were used in
[
107
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
]

to obtain identities within special classes of modular graph functions. An efficient Mathematica package for
algebraic and differential relations was developed in
[

,
113
]
. A class of related modular functions is presented in
[

,
115
]
.

Fourier series expansions were obtained in
[

,

,

,
117
]
, while Poincaré series were derived in
[

,
98
,

. The role of modular graph functions in genus-one amplitudes for open and Heterotic strings was elucidated in
[

. The solutions to the differential identities obtained from holomorphic subgraph reduction may be obtained in terms of iterated elliptic integrals and systematically represented in terms of generating functions
[

The generalization of modular graph functions to higher genera was initiated in

]
and is directly motivated by the study of the low energy expansion of superstring amplitudes, as will be discussed extensively in section

. The natural generalization of modular graph forms to higher genus is provided by modular graph tensors, which were introduced in
