Part II

Extensions and Applications

In the second part of these lecture notes, we consider mathematical and physical extensions and applications of the material discussed in the first part. We shall also exhibit the connections with the second part of the title, namely various topics in string theory and related fields, wherever appropriate.

We start off in section
10
with a review of Hecke operators, Hecke eigenforms, and the action of Hecke operators on Maass forms and vector-valued modular forms. We close with a physical application to the study of two-dimensional conformal field theory. In section
11
, we consider the topic of complex multiplication and singular moduli. We prove that the modular
j
-function is an algebraic integer at complex multiplication points
\tau
and evaluate elliptic and
\vartheta
-functions at these points.

In section
12
, we present a quick introduction to string amplitudes. We review their perturbative expansion in the genus which corresponds to the loop expansion in quantum field theory, and the importance of conformal symmetry in decoupling of negative norm states. We summarize results for amplitudes to tree-level, one-loop, and two-loop orders without derivation. We emphasize the crucial role played by modular invariance in the UV finiteness of string theory, and by modular graph functions in the low energy expansion in effective interactions. As an application, we analyze toroidal compactifications in section
13
.

In section
14
we review Type IIB supergravity and superstring theory, discuss its S-duality symmetry under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, spell out the modular properties of the low energy effective interactions, and make contact with the low energy effective interactions predicted earlier from superstring amplitudes in section
12
.

In section
15
we present a brief introduction to Yang-Mills theories with extended supersymmetry in four space-time dimensions. We review Goddard-Nuyts-Olive duality, its concrete realization as Montonen-Olive dualities in
{\cal N}=4
theories, and then discuss the Seiberg-Witten solution with emphasis on the role played by
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. We close with a discussion of dualities of
{\cal N}=2
superconformal Yang-Mills theory.

In the last section
16
, we address Galois theory, along with some applications to rational conformal field theory.

10

Hecke Theory

A natural set of operators acting on the space of modular forms are the Hecke operators
{\sf T}_{n}
, labeled by positive integers
n
. They map holomorphic functions to holomorphic functions, weight-
k
modular forms to weight-
k
modular forms, and weight-
k
cusp forms to weight-
k
cusp forms. For the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
the Hecke operators are endomorphisms that map the space of holomorphic modular forms
{\cal M}_{k}
into itself, and map the subspace of cusp forms
{\cal S}_{k}
into itself. For congruence subgroups, the Hecke operators map weight-
k
modular forms of one congruence subgroup into those of another congruence subgroup. Hecke operators commute with the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the upper half-plane so that non-holomorphic Eisenstein series and cusp forms are simultaneous eigenfunctions of all Hecke operators. Finally, given a modular form with positive integer Fourier coefficients, the Hecke transforms also have positive integer Fourier coefficients. For this reason, Hecke operators are relevant in a number of physical problems, as we shall see shortly.

10.1

Definition of Hecke operators

Given a lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{1}+{\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{2}
with modulus
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}\in{\cal H}
, a modular form
f(\tau)
of weight
k
under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
may be represented by a degree
k
homogeneous function of the lattice
\Lambda
, denoted by the same letter,
f(\Lambda)=\omega_{1}^{-k}f(\omega_{2}/\omega_{1})
. Modularity of
f(\tau)
is then equivalent to the fact that
f(\Lambda)
is intrinsic and depends only on the lattice
\Lambda
, but not on the particular basis vectors
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
used to represent
\Lambda
. The Hecke operator
{\sf T}_{n}:{\cal M}_{k}\rightarrow{\cal M}_{k}
may now be defined on
f(\Lambda)
as a sum over all index
n
sub-lattices
\Lambda_{n}\subset\Lambda
,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\Lambda)=n^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda_{n}\subset\Lambda\\
[\Lambda:\Lambda_{n}]=n\end{subarray}}f(\Lambda_{n})

(10.1.1)

where the sub-lattice
\Lambda_{n}
is understood to be a subgroup of
\Lambda
. The definition of
{\sf T}_{n}
given above is intrinsic, in that it depends only on the lattices
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{n}
, and not on any particular choice of the basis vectors for each lattice. An arbitrary multiplicative normalization factor, which depends only on
k
and
n
, is allowed in the definition of
{\sf T}_{n}
, and has been chosen here for later convenience.

To make contact with modular forms expressed as functions of
\tau\in{\cal H}
, we now choose specific basis vectors for both
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{n}
. In particular, the set of index
n
sub-lattices
\Lambda_{n}
may be parametrized by choosing basis vectors
\omega_{1}^{(n)}
,
\omega_{2}^{(n)}
for
\Lambda_{n}
which are integer linear combinations of the basis vectors
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
of
\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\omega_{2}^{(n)}=a\,\omega_{2}+b\,\omega_{1}\hskip 36.135pt\omega_{1}^{(n)}=c\,\omega_{2}+d\,\omega_{1}

(10.1.2)

where
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{n}
are Abelian groups,
\Lambda_{n}
is a normal subgroup of
\Lambda
and the quotient
\Lambda/\Lambda_{n}
is a finite group. One may represent
\Lambda/\Lambda_{n}
as the set of the points in
\Lambda
modulo
\Lambda_{n}
, equipped with addition modulo
\Lambda_{n}
. The cardinality of
\Lambda/\Lambda_{n}
is the index
[\Lambda:\Lambda_{n}]
which is given by the number of points in
\Lambda
that belong in the fundamental parallelogram of
\Lambda_{n}
, and this number is
n=ad-bc
, a number that is positive assuming that
\Lambda_{n}
inherits its orientation from
\Lambda
. The above parametrization of
\Lambda_{n}
motivates us to introduce the set
M_{n}
of all
2\times 2
matrices with integer coefficients of determinant
n>0
,

\displaystyle M_{n}=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)\Big|\,\,a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}};\,\,ad-bc=n>0\right\}

(10.1.3)

Given two matrices
A,B\in M_{n}
, one introduces an equivalence relation
A\sim B
whenever
A=\mu B
for some element
\mu\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The quotient
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
is the set of equivalence classes of elements of
M_{n}
, subject to the equivalence relation. This relation is precisely the equivalence relation between lattices
\Lambda_{n}
under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations of the underlying lattice
\Lambda
. Therefore, the cosets in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the lattices
\Lambda_{n}
and the Hecke operator
{\sf T}_{n}
may equivalently be defined to act as follows,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=n^{k-1}\sum_{\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}}(c\tau+d)^{-k}f\left(\gamma\tau\right)

(10.1.4)

The above definition makes it clear that
{\sf T}_{n}
maps holomorphic functions to holomorphic functions. Below we shall show that it maps modular forms of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
to modular forms.

10.2

Explicit parametrization of equivalence classes
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}

The action of the Hecke operators
{\sf T}_{n}
can be made even more explicit by using the following convenient explicit parametrization of the equivalence classes
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
.

Lemma 10.1

Every equivalence class in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
contains an upper triangular representative of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{n}
, with
d>0
. A complete set of inequivalent elements of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
is given by
b\in\{0,1,\cdots,d-1\}
.

To prove the first part of the Lemma, we show that for every matrix
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{n}
there exists a
\mu\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
\mu A
is of the form given in the Lemma. To do so we parameterize
\mu
as follows,

\displaystyle\mu=\left(\begin{matrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\mu A=\left(\begin{matrix}pa+qc&pb+qd\\
ra+sc&rb+sd\end{matrix}\right)

(10.2.1)

If
c=0
then
d\not=0
in view of
ad-bc=n>0
by the definition of
M_{n}
and we set
r=q=0
and
p=s={\rm sign}(d)
to obtain a representative of the desired form. For the remaining cases
c\neq 0
we choose mutually prime
r
and
s
such that
s/r=-a/c
to render the matrix
\mu A
upper triangular. By Bézout’s theorem (reviewed in appendix
A
) there exist integers
p
and
q
such that
ps-qr=1
guarantees
\mu\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and either
\mu
or
-\mu
gives the desired matrix.

To prove the second part of the Lemma, we show that the set given by
b\in\{0,1,\cdots,d-1\}
is complete and that all such elements are inequivalent under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. To see that the set is complete, we take two matrices
A_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b_{1}\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
A_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b_{2}\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with
b_{1}\equiv b_{2}\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,d)
. Then if
b_{1}=b_{2}+kd
we define
\mu=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&k\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
so that
A_{1}=\mu A_{2}
, and hence
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
. Conversely, to see that all elements are inequivalent we proceed as follows. If
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are such that
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
, there exists
\mu=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
A_{1}=\mu A_{2}
, namely,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&d_{1}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}a_{2}&b_{2}\\
0&d_{2}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}pa_{2}&pb_{2}+qd_{2}\\
ra_{2}&rb_{2}+sd_{2}\end{matrix}\right)

(10.2.2)

Since
a_{2}d_{2}=n
, clearly
a_{2}\neq 0
, and hence by equating the bottom-left entries on both sides we conclude that
r=0
. Then since
pq-rs=1
, we have
pq=1
and thus
p=q=\pm 1
. We make the choice
p=q=+1
, which we can always do by replacing
\mu
with
-\mu
if needed. Then equating entries on both sides, we see that
a_{1}=a_{2}
,
d_{1}=d_{2}
, and
b_{1}\equiv b_{2}\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,d)
. Hence if we restrict to
b\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
, there are no equivalences.

We shall also make use of the following simple but important lemma.

Lemma 10.2

For every pair
(A_{1},\mu_{1})
with
A_{1}\in M_{n}
and
\mu_{1}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, there exists a pair
(A_{2},\mu_{2})
with
A_{2}\in M_{n}
and
\mu_{2}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
A_{1}\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}A_{2}
. As a result, the left and right cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
and
M_{n}/SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
are isomorphic.

To prove this lemma, we may choose
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
of the form provided by Lemma
10.1
without loss of generality, and parametrize the matrices
A_{1},A_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
involved as follows,

\displaystyle A_{1}=\left(\begin{matrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&d_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 28.90755ptA_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}a_{2}&b_{2}\\
0&d_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 28.90755pt\mu_{1}=\left(\begin{matrix}p_{1}&q_{1}\\
r_{1}&s_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 28.90755pt\mu_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}p_{2}&q_{2}\\
r_{2}&s_{2}\end{matrix}\right)

(10.2.3)

Given
\mu_{1}
and
A_{1}
, one proceeds to solve for
A_{2}
and
\mu_{2}
using
\mu_{2}A_{2}=A_{1}\mu_{1}
in components,

\displaystyle p_{2}a_{2}

\displaystyle=a_{1}p_{1}+b_{1}r_{1}

\displaystyle p_{2}b_{2}+q_{2}d_{2}

\displaystyle=a_{1}q_{1}+b_{1}s_{1}

\displaystyle r_{2}a_{2}

\displaystyle=d_{1}r_{1}

\displaystyle r_{2}b_{2}+s_{2}d_{2}

\displaystyle=d_{1}s_{1}

(10.2.4)

From the equations on the left one obtains
p_{2}/r_{2}=(a_{1}p_{1}+b_{1}r_{1})/(d_{1}r_{1})
, which determines the pair
(p_{2},r_{2})
uniquely up to a common sign in view of the fact that
p_{2},r_{2}
must be relatively prime. The entry
a_{2}
is then determined by
a_{2}=d_{1}r_{1}/r_{2}
. Having determined the pair
p_{2},r_{2}
, Bézout’s theorem guarantees the existence of a solution
q_{2},s_{2}
to
p_{2}s_{2}-q_{2}r_{2}=1
. In terms of these solutions, we readily solve the equations on the right to obtain,

\displaystyle b_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{2}a_{1}q_{1}+s_{2}b_{1}s_{1}-q_{2}d_{1}s_{1}

\displaystyle d_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{2}d_{1}s_{1}-r_{2}a_{1}q_{1}-r_{2}b_{1}s_{1}

(10.2.5)

10.3

Hecke operators map
{\cal M}_{k}
to
{\cal M}_{k}

Lemma
10.1
allows us to recast the action of the Hecke operators more explicitly as follows,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=n^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad=n\\
d>0\end{subarray}}{1\over d^{k}}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}f\left({a\tau+b\over d}\right)

(10.3.1)

or by solving for
a=n/d
,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=n^{k-1}\sum_{{d|n\atop d>0}}{1\over d^{k}}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}f\left({n\tau+bd\over d^{2}}\right)

(10.3.2)

In terms of these expressions for
{\sf T}_{n}
, it is now straightforward to prove the modularity of
{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)
. We shall use the notations and parametrizations of Lemma
10.2
. Indeed, considering a transformation by
\mu_{1}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we have,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\mu_{1}\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{k-1}\sum_{A_{1}}{1\over d_{1}^{k}}\,f\left(A_{1}\mu_{1}\tau\right)

(10.3.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{k-1}\sum_{A_{2}}{1\over d_{1}^{k}}\,f\left(\mu_{2}A_{2}\tau\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{k-1}\sum_{A_{2}}{1\over d_{1}^{k}}\,(r_{2}A_{2}\tau+s_{2})^{k}f\left(A_{2}\tau\right)

where we have introduced a shorthand notation
\sum_{A}
for the summations in (
10.3.1
), and have used the fact that if
A_{1}
runs through a complete set of inequivalent elements of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
, so too does
A_{2}
. Substituting the two equations on the second line of (
10.2
), we then obtain

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\mu_{1}\tau)=n^{k-1}\sum_{A_{2}}{1\over d_{2}^{k}}(r_{1}\tau+s_{1})^{k}f\left(A_{2}\tau\right)=(r_{1}\tau+s_{1})^{k}\,{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)

(10.3.4)

which is the desired result.

10.4

Fourier expansions

For many applications in Physics and Mathematics, we will often be interested in the Fourier expansions of Hecke transforms. Given a weight-
k
modular function
f(\tau)
with Fourier expansion
f(\tau)=\sum_{m}a_{m}q^{m}
, the Fourier expansion of the Hecke transform can be computed explicitly as follows.
We begin by noting that,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=n^{k-1}\sum_{d|n}{1\over d^{k}}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{m}\,q^{nm\over d^{2}}e^{2\pi i{bm\over d}}\,=\,\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\sum_{d|\gcd(n,m)}\left({n\over d}\right)^{k-1}a_{m}\,q^{nm\over d^{2}}

(10.4.1)

The second equality follows from the fact that
\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}e^{2\pi i{bm\over d}}
equals
d
if
{m\over d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and vanishes otherwise. Then we simply make repeated changes to the summation index to get,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{d|n}\left({n\over d}\right)^{k-1}a_{\ell d}\,q^{n\ell\over d}=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{t|n}t^{k-1}a_{\ell n/t}\,q^{\ell t}

(10.4.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\sum_{t|{\gcd}(m,n)}t^{k-1}a_{mn/t^{2}}\,q^{m}

We thus conclude that the Fourier expansion of the Hecke transform is given by,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b^{(n)}_{m}q^{m}\hskip 72.26999ptb^{(n)}_{m}=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(m,n)}d^{k-1}a_{mn/d^{2}}

(10.4.3)

If the Fourier coefficients
a_{m}
of
f(\tau)
are integers, then by construction so are the Fourier coefficients of the Hecke transform
{\sf T}_{n}f
for all
n>0
. Furthermore, we see from (
10.4.3
) that if
a_{0}=0
then
b^{(n)}_{0}=0
as well, and thus
{\sf T}_{n}
acts as an endomorphism on the space
{\cal S}_{k}
of weight-
k
cusp forms.

In the particular case of
n=p
being prime, the Fourier coefficients
b^{(n)}_{m}
of the Hecke transform take a particularly simple form,

\displaystyle b^{(p)}_{m}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\,\,p^{k}a_{pm}&\hskip 21.68121ptp\nmid m\\
\,\,p^{k-1}(p\,a_{pm}+a_{m/p})&\hskip 21.68121ptp\,|\,m\end{matrix}\right.

(10.4.4)

If
k\geq 1
, we see that if
a_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
b^{(p)}_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
as well. For
k=0
though this is no longer the case. For this reason, in the case of
k=0
one usually drops the conventional factor of
n^{k-1}
in (
10.1.4
).

10.5

Example: the Ramanujan tau function

We illustrate the utility of this machinery by means of a simple example. In particular, let us prove the product formulas (
3.8.16
) and (
3.8.17
) for the Ramanujan tau function, defined in (
3.8.13
) of section
3.8.1
, and repeated here for convenience,

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau)=(2\pi)^{12}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tau(n)q^{n}

(10.5.1)

To do so, we apply the Hecke operators to the discriminant
\Delta(\tau)
to obtain,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}\Delta(\tau)=\sum_{m}b_{m}^{(n)}q^{m}\hskip 57.81621ptb_{m}^{(n)}=(2\pi)^{12}\sum_{d|{\gcd}(m,n)}d^{11}\,\tau\left({nm\over d^{2}}\right)

(10.5.2)

Since
\Delta(\tau)
and
{\sf T}_{n}\Delta(\tau)
are both holomorphic cusp forms of weight 12, and the space
{\cal S}_{12}
is one-dimensional, we conclude that we must have,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}\Delta(\tau)=\alpha_{n}\Delta(\tau)

(10.5.3)

for some sequence of constants
\alpha_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Equating Fourier coefficients using (
10.4.3
), we obtain the following relation,

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}\tau(m)=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(m,n)}d^{11}\,\tau\left({nm\over d^{2}}\right)

(10.5.4)

For the particular case of
m=1
we have
\tau(1)=1
and only the term with
d=1
contributes to the sum on the right side, so that
\alpha_{n}=\tau(n)
. Substituting this expression for
\alpha_{n}
into (
10.5.4
) readily reproduces (
3.8.17
).

10.6

Multiplicative properties of Hecke operators

An important property of Hecke operators is their behavior under multiplication,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}{\sf T}_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|(n,m)}d^{k-1}\,{\sf T}_{mn/d^{2}}

(10.6.1)

The full proof of this property is straightforward and standard (see e.g.
[
128
]
), but somewhat lengthy. Here we settle for a proof of a special case of this relation,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}{\sf T}_{m}={\sf T}_{nm}\hskip 57.81621pt\gcd(m,n)=1

(10.6.2)

To prove this, we begin by explicitly applying the Hecke operators on a weight-
k
modular form
f(\tau)
to obtain

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}{\sf T}_{m}f(\tau)=(nm)^{k-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\delta=n\\
\delta>0\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad=m\\
d>0\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{\beta=0}^{\delta-1}\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}{1\over(d\delta)^{k}}f\left({\alpha a\tau+(\alpha b+\beta d)\over d\delta}\right)

(10.6.3)

Now we make use of the fact that
(m,n)=1
, which tells us that since
d
and
\delta
run through positive divisors of
n
and
m
respectively,
d\delta
should run through the positive divisors of the product
nm
.
Furthermore, since
b

({\rm mod~}d)
runs through a complete set of representatives for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{m}
and
\beta

({\rm mod~}\delta)
runs through a full set of representatives for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
, then
\alpha b+\beta d

({\rm mod~}d\delta)
runs through a complete set of inequivalent representatives for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{mn}
. Hence by the definition given in (
10.1.4
), the right side of (
10.6.3
) is exactly the action of
{\sf T}_{mn}
on
f(\tau)
.

10.7

Hecke eigenforms

The multiplication identity (
10.6.1
) implies that the Hecke operators commute. As such, we may consider modular forms
f(\tau)\in{\cal M}_{k}
which are simultaneous eigenfunctions of all Hecke operators,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=\lambda_{n}f(\tau)\hskip 36.135pt\forall\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}

(10.7.1)

Such forms are called Hecke eigenforms, or just ‘‘eigenforms” for short.
21
21

21

In some literature the term eigenform refers to eigenfunctions of only a single Hecke operator, whereas our definition of eigenforms would be referred to as “simultaneous eigenforms.”
Comparing the
{\cal O}(q)
terms in the Fourier expansions on both sides of (
10.7.1
), the Fourier coefficients of eigenforms are seen to satisfy,

\displaystyle b_{m}^{(n)}=\lambda_{n}a_{m}

(10.7.2)

using (
10.4.3
). Considering in particular
m=1
, we see that
a_{n}=\lambda_{n}a_{1}
, and thus that any non-trivial eigenform must have
a_{1}\neq 0
. One often finds it useful to normalize
a_{1}=1
so that the Hecke eigenvalues coincide with the Fourier coefficients of
f(\tau)
,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)=a_{n}f(\tau)

(10.7.3)

It is a general fact that normalized Hecke eigenforms have Fourier coefficients which are real (by Hermiticity of
{\sf T}_{n}
, as will be discussed momentarily) and multiplicative,

\displaystyle a_{m}a_{n}=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(m,n)}d^{k-1}a_{mn/d^{2}}

(10.7.4)

The existence of eigenforms is obvious for certain weights. For example, for weights
k=4,6,8,10,
and
14
, we saw in Theorem
3.3
that the space
{\cal M}_{k}
is one-dimensional, being generated by the relevant Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
. Hence for such values of
k
the Hecke transform
{\sf T}_{n}{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
must clearly be a multiple of
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
for any
n
, making
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
a Hecke eigenform. More surprisingly, the Eisenstein series are Hecke eigenforms even when
{\rm dim}\,{\cal M}_{k}>1
, which is shown as follows.
Consider a generic non-cuspidal modular function satisfying

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f=\lambda_{n}f

(10.7.5)

with
f
having even weight
k
. For non-cuspidal
f
the Fourier coefficient
a_{0}
is non-zero, and by extension
b_{0}^{(n)}
is non-zero as well.
In particular, for
m=0,1
we have from (
10.4.3
) that

\displaystyle b_{0}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}d^{k-1}a_{0}\,\,\,=\,\,\,a_{0}\,\sigma_{k-1}(n)

\displaystyle b_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|{\gcd}(1,n)}d^{k-1}a_{n/d^{2}}\,\,\,=\,\,\,a_{n}

(10.7.6)

with
\sigma_{*}(n)
the sum of divisors function. Comparing the first of these to (
10.7.2
) allows us to conclude that
\lambda_{n}=\sigma_{k-1}(n)
, and then from the second of these we obtain
a_{n}=a_{1}\sigma_{k-1}(n)
. Choosing normalization
a_{0}=1
, the Fourier expansion of the Hecke eigenform
f(\tau)
is required to take the following form,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=1+a_{1}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}(N)\,q^{N}

(10.7.7)

The normalized Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
take exactly this form, as in (
3.5.13
). Incidentally, note that the divisor function indeed satisfies the multiplicative property (
10.7.4
).

Besides Eisenstein series, all Hecke eigenforms are cuspidal. We have already encountered one example of a cuspidal eigenform in section
10.5
, where we saw that the modular discriminant
\Delta(\tau)
is an eigenfunction of the Hecke operators, with the eigenvalues being precisely the values of the Ramanujan tau function. We also saw that the Ramanujan tau function satisfied a multiplicativity property consistent with (
10.7.4
).

The fact that
\Delta(\tau)
was an eigenform followed simply from the fact that
{\rm dim}\,{\cal S}_{12}=1
. The same argument holds for cusp forms of weight
k=16,18,20,22,
and
26
, where
{\rm dim}\,{\cal S}_{k}=1
, and is generated by
\Delta(\tau){\sf E}_{k}(\tau)
. However, it is a remarkable fact, due to Hecke, that there exist cuspidal eigenforms for other
k
as well. In fact, Hecke showed that eigenforms provide a basis of
{\cal M}_{k}
for every
k
. In order to prove this, he found it necessary to introduce an inner product on the space of cusp forms, as we now briefly review.

10.7.1

Petersson inner product

Weight-
k
cusp forms admit an inner product known as the Petersson inner product,

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash\mathbb{H}}{d\tau_{1}d\tau_{2}\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,(\tau_{2})^{k}f(\tau)\overline{g(\tau)}

(10.7.8)

which is convergent due to the vanishing of
f
or
g
towards the cusp.
A key fact is that Hecke operators are self-adjoint under this inner product,

\displaystyle\langle{\sf T}_{n}f,g\rangle=\langle f,{\sf T}_{n}g\rangle

(10.7.9)

This together with commutativity of
{\sf T}_{n}
implies that a generic
f\in{\cal S}_{k}
can be decomposed into a basis of Hecke eigenforms, which is the result quoted before. This also tells us that the eigenvalues of
{\sf T}_{n}
(and hence the Fourier coefficients of normalized eigenforms) are all real—another result quoted earlier.

10.8

Hecke operators acting on Maass forms

We now review the properties of Hecke operators acting on the space of Maass forms of weight 0, which was introduced in section
4.3
. Recall that a Maass form
f(\tau)
is a complex-valued function of
\tau
that satisfies a Laplace eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle\Delta f(\tau)=s(s-1)f(\tau)

(10.8.1)

is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and has at most polynomial growth at the cusp. The Fourier series for general Maass forms was obtained in (
4.3.9
), and we repeat it here for convenience,

\displaystyle f(\tau)=a\,\tau_{2}^{s}+b\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}+\sum_{N\not=0}\alpha_{N}\,\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,K_{s-1/2}(2\pi|N|\tau_{2})\,e^{2\pi iN\tau_{1}}

(10.8.2)

The space of all Maass forms for the full modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
and with eigenvalue
s(s-1)
is denoted
{\cal N}(s)={\cal N}(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}}),s)
with the understanding that it satisfies
{\cal N}(1-s)={\cal N}(s)
. Non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{s}(\tau)
, defined and normalized in (
4.2.1
), are Maass forms whose Fourier series were obtained in (
4.2.16
),

\displaystyle a={2\zeta(2s)\over\pi^{s}}\hskip 36.135ptb={2\Gamma(s-\tfrac{1}{2})\zeta(2s-1)\over\Gamma(s)\,\pi^{s-\tfrac{1}{2}}}\hskip 36.135pt\alpha_{N}={4|N|^{\tfrac{1}{2}-s}\sigma_{2s-1}(|N|)\over\Gamma(s)}

(10.8.3)

A Maass form
f(\tau)
is a cusp form provided the constant Fourier mode in (
10.8.2
) vanishes,
a=b=0
. Cusp forms decay exponentially at the cusp and have finite norm with respect to the Petersson inner product. The space of cusp forms is denoted
{\cal S}(s)
. The space
{\cal S}(s)
is empty except for an infinite number of discrete eigenvalues of the form
s\in\tfrac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
. Theorem
4.2
provides a basis for
{\cal N}(s)
in terms of Eisenstein series and cusp forms.

Hecke operators are linear operators that may be defined on Maass forms in
{\cal N}(s)
similarly to how they were defined on holomorphic modular forms,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)={1\over\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}}f(\gamma\tau)

(10.8.4)

In contrast to (
10.1.4
), there is no power of
(c\tau+d)
multiplying the summand as the form
f(\tau)
has weight 0. The definition allows for an arbitrary normalization prefactor that depends only on
n
and is completely multiplicative and has been chosen to be
1/\sqrt{n}
for later convenience. Using Lemma
10.2
, and the fact that a Maass form is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, it follows immediately that
{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)
is also invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Furthermore, since
f(\tau)
has at most polynomial growth at the cusp, so must
{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)
. This is because it is defined to be a finite sum of terms, each of which has at most polynomial growth. By manipulations analogous to those used for the holomorphic case, one may use an explicit parametrization of the coset
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
to recast the action of the Hecke operators in the following form,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)={1\over\sqrt{n}}\sum_{{ad=n,d>0\atop b=0,\cdots,d-1}}f\left({a\tau+b\over d}\right)

(10.8.5)

The Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta=4\tau_{2}^{2}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
is invariant under
{\sf T}_{n}
because the transformation
\tau\to\tau^{\prime}=(a\tau+d)/d
implies
\tau_{2}\to\tau_{2}^{\prime}=a\tau_{2}/d
and
\partial_{\tau}\to\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}=d/a\,\partial_{\tau}
. These results may be summarized and further extended in the following theorem
[
20
]
:

Theorem 10.3

The Hecke operators
{\sf T}_{n}
satisfy the following properties.

1.

{\sf T}_{n}
map
{\cal N}(s)
to
{\cal N}(s)
and
{\cal S}(s)
to
{\cal S}(s)
.

2.

The Fourier series of a Maass form
f(\tau)
in
{\cal N}(s)
, given in (
10.8.2
), and its Hecke image
{\sf T}_{n}f(\tau)
expressed in the form given in (
10.8.2
) with
a\to a^{\prime},b\to b^{\prime},\alpha_{N}\to\alpha_{N}^{\prime}
are related as follows,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}a^{\prime}=n^{1/2-s}\sigma_{2s-1}(n)\,a\cr b^{\prime}=n^{s-1/2}\sigma_{1-2s}(n)\,b\end{matrix}\right.\hskip 72.26999pt\alpha_{N}^{\prime}=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(N,n)}\alpha_{Nn/d^{2}}

(10.8.6)

3.

The product of operators
{\sf T}_{m}
and
{\sf T}_{n}
on
{\cal N}(s)
is commutative and given as follows,

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{m}{\sf T}_{n}=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(m,n)}{\sf T}_{mn/d^{2}}

(10.8.7)

4.

The operators
{\sf T}_{n}
are self-adjoint linear operators with respect to the Petersson inner product on the space of cusp forms, and may be simultaneously diagonalized on
{\cal S}(s)
.

5.

If
f\in{\cal N}(s)
is a simultaneous eigenfunction of all Hecke operators with eigenvalues
{\sf T}_{n}f=\lambda_{n}f
then the coefficients
\alpha_{N}
in its Fourier expansion (
10.8.2
) satisfy,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\pm N}=\lambda_{N}\,\alpha_{\pm 1}\hskip 72.26999ptN>0

(10.8.8)

Therefore, up to the normalizations
\alpha_{\pm 1}
, the coefficients of the non-constant Fourier modes are the eigenvalues of the Hecke operators.

Item 1 was already established in the preamble to the theorem, while Item 2 readily follows by identifying terms in the Fourier series for
f
and
{\sf T}_{n}f
. Item 3 may be established along the same lines as the product formula (
10.6.1
) was for holomorphic modular forms. The proof of Item 4 is given in
[
20
]
. Finally, Item 5 is established using the second relation in Item 2 for
\alpha_{N}^{\prime}=\lambda_{n}\alpha_{N}
, which implies,

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\,\alpha_{N}=\sum_{d|{\gcd}(N,n)}\alpha_{Nn/d^{2}}

(10.8.9)

for all
n>0
and all
N\not=0
. Setting
N=\pm 1
collapses the sum on the right to just the term
d=1
, which produces the result announced.

10.9

Hecke operators on vvmfs

In section
8
we introduced modular differential equations (MDEs), and discussed how they were usefully organized according to the so-called Wronskian index
\ell
, defined in (
8.3.22
), which gives a rough measure of the number of poles allowed in the coefficient functions. For the case of
\ell=0
the coefficient functions must be holomorphic, and for low orders the MDEs can be recast as hypergeometric equations and solved exactly, as was done in sections
8.2.2
and
8.2.3
for the cases of second- and third order, respectively.

Obtaining exact solutions to MDEs for general
\ell
is more difficult. One way to obtain (a subset of) solutions to MDEs with non-zero
\ell
is to make use of Hecke operators. Here we will give only a flavor of how this is done. Above we have defined the action of Hecke operators on modular forms for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. In fact, there exists a straightforward generalization to modular forms for congruence subgroups. Assuming the Integrality Conjecture of section
8.3.2
, this then leads to a component-wise action of the Hecke operators on integral vector-valued modular forms. For a vector-valued modular form
{\bf f}(\tau)
with components
f_{i}(\tau)
transforming covariantly under
\Gamma(N)
, the action of
{\sf T}_{p}
for
p
prime such that
(p,N)=1
is given by

\displaystyle({\sf T}_{p}f)_{i}(\tau)=\sum_{j}\rho_{ij}(\sigma_{p})f_{j}(p\tau)+\sum_{b=0}^{p-1}f_{i}\left({\tau+bN\over p}\right)

(10.9.1)

where
\rho(\sigma_{p})
is a matrix representation of

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}=T^{\overline{p}}S^{-1}T^{p}ST^{\overline{p}}S

(10.9.2)

and
\overline{p}
is the modular inverse of
p
, i.e.
p\overline{p}\equiv 1
(mod
N
). This definition of the Hecke operators is chosen to lead to a simple action on the Fourier coefficients. Indeed, if we write the Fourier expansion of the components of a vector-valued modular function as,

\displaystyle f_{i}(\tau)=\sum_{n}a_{n}^{(i)}q^{n/N}

(10.9.3)

then one has that

\displaystyle({\sf T}_{p}f)_{i}(\tau)=\sum_{n}b_{n}^{(i)}(p)q^{n/N}

(10.9.4)

with

\displaystyle b_{n}^{(i)}(p)=\left\{\begin{matrix}pa_{pn}^{(i)}&\,\,\,\,p\nmid n\\
pa_{pn}^{(i)}+\sum_{j}\rho_{ij}(\sigma_{p})a_{n/p}^{(j)}&\,\,\,\,p\,|\,\,n\end{matrix}\right.

(10.9.5)

This action of the Hecke operator gives a new vector-valued modular form with components which have the same weight
k
under
\Gamma(N)
as before, but now with different leading exponents
n_{0}^{(i)}
in the notation of (
8.3.15
). Because
\mathrm{ord}_{W_{d}}(i\infty)=\sum_{i}n_{0}^{(i)}
, we expect from (
8.3.23
) that the Hecke image
{\sf T}_{p}{\bf f}(\tau)
generically satisfies an MDE with different Wronskian index
\ell
. The Hecke operators can thus be used to transform solutions to MDEs with
\ell=0
to solutions to those with
\ell>0
.

We content ourselves with a single example here. Consider the three-dimensional vector-valued modular function made up of characters of the Ising CFT, given in (
8.2.4
). From (
8.2.4
), the leading exponents are seen to be

\displaystyle n_{0}^{(1)}={23\over 48}\hskip 36.135ptn_{0}^{(2)}=-{1\over 48}\hskip 36.135ptn_{0}^{(3)}={1\over 24}

(10.9.6)

in the notation of (
8.3.15
). Upon application of the Hecke operator
{\sf T}_{p}
, these change to

\displaystyle\tilde{n}_{0}^{(1)}={23p\,\,{\rm mod}\,48\over 48}\hskip 36.135pt\tilde{n}_{0}^{(2)}=-{p\over 48}\hskip 36.135pt\tilde{n}_{0}^{(3)}={2p\,\,{\rm mod}\,48\over 48}

(10.9.7)

from (
10.9.5
). Then using the formula (
8.3.23
), we conclude that for e.g.
{\sf T}_{49},{\sf T}_{53}
, and
{\sf T}_{55}
we have
\ell=6
, and hence application of these Hecke operators to the Ising characters gives solutions to an MDE of order
3
with meromorphic coefficient functions. These solutions are exact in the sense that their full Fourier expansions can be obtained, though they are not known to be expressible in terms of any standard functions.

Another interesting case is that of
{\sf T}_{47}
acting on the Ising characters. In that case we obtain

\displaystyle({\sf T}_{47}\,\chi_{1})(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-{47\over 48}}\left(1+96256\,q^{2}+9646891\,q^{3}+366845011\,q^{4}+\dots\right)

(10.9.8)

\displaystyle({\sf T}_{47}\,\chi_{\epsilon})(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{25\over 48}\left(4371+1143745\,q+64680601\,q^{2}+1829005611\,q^{3}+\dots\right)

\displaystyle({\sf T}_{47}\,\chi_{\sigma})(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{23\over 24}\left(96256+10602496\,q+420831232\,q^{2}+9685952512\,q^{3}+\dots\right)

These turn out to be solutions to an order
3
MDE with
\ell=0
, and can be shown to be precisely the characters of the so-called Baby Monster CFT, which has as its global symmetry group the Baby Monster
\mathbb{B}
, the second-largest finite sporadic group. Hence the Hecke operator
{\sf T}_{47}
maps from the Ising CFT to the Baby Monster CFT, in some appropriate sense.

10.10

Physics applications

We now give a physical application of Hecke theory. Before doing so, we will need to recall the basic notion of Virasoro characters. In section
A.7
, we introduced characters for finite groups. Characters can be introduced for Lie groups as well, in which case, given a representation
{\cal R}
of the group, we have,

\displaystyle\chi_{{\cal R}}(g)={\rm Tr}_{{\cal R}}\,(g)

(10.10.1)

with the trace
{\rm Tr}_{\cal R}
taken in representation space of
{\cal R}
. The general theory of characters is rich, but largely unnecessary for our purposes. The particular case of importance to us here is that of the characters of the Virasoro group. As we have discussed in section
5.1.1
, the Virasoro group is an infinite-dimensional Lie group which is the universal central extension of the group of diffeomorphisms of the circle. The corresponding Lie algebra is the Virasoro algebra, whose generators
L_{n}
satisfy the structure relations given in (
5.1.3
), which we repeat here for convenience,

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+{\mathfrak{c}\over 12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n,-m}

(10.10.2)

The parameter
\mathfrak{c}
here is known as the central charge.

A quantum field theory with Virasoro symmetry is known as a conformal field theory (CFT). By “symmetry,” one means that the state space of the theory is organized into Virasoro representations. In a unitary CFT, these representations can be constructed by beginning with a highest weight state
|h\rangle
satisfying

\displaystyle L_{0}|h\rangle=h|h\rangle\hskip 36.135ptL_{n}|h\rangle=0\hskip 21.68121ptn>0

(10.10.3)

and then generating other states in the representation by taking linear combinations of states of the form,

\displaystyle L_{-n_{1}}L_{-n_{2}}\dots L_{-n_{r}}|h\rangle\hskip 36.135ptn_{i}>0

(10.10.4)

Such states are known as descendants, and will be collectively denoted by
|h,\{n\}\rangle
. The character associated to a representation labelled by
h
is then defined to be,

\displaystyle\chi_{h}(\tau)=q^{h-{\mathfrak{c}\over 24}}\sum_{\{n\}}q^{N}

(10.10.5)

with
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
and
N=\sum_{i}n_{i}
. This is sometimes also written as,

\displaystyle\chi_{h}(\tau)={\rm Tr}_{{\cal V}_{h}}\left(q^{L_{0}-{\mathfrak{c}\over 24}}\right)

(10.10.6)

with
{\cal V}_{h}
known as the weight-h Verma module.

Characters play an important role in the study of 2d CFTs. In particular, the torus partition function of many CFTs can be decomposed into a sum over conformal families and a sum within each family, giving

\displaystyle Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{h,\tilde{h}}N_{h,{\tilde{h}}}\,\chi_{h}(\tau)\overline{\chi_{\tilde{h}}(\tau)}

(10.10.7)

for some gluing matrix
N_{h,{\tilde{h}}}
. Here we are assuming that the indices
h,\tilde{h}
are discrete, but not necessarily that the sums over conformal families are finite.

The partition function is by definition a weight-0 modular function. But conversely, not every modular function corresponds to a valid CFT partition function. For example, since partition functions count degeneracies of states, any physically sensible partition function must have positive integer Fourier coefficients. The same is true for the constituent characters. In this context, it is useful to introduce a variant of the Hecke operators with alternative normalization,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{n}:=n\,{\sf T}_{n}

(10.10.8)

which has the virtue that if our original function
\chi(\tau)
has positive integer Fourier coefficients
a_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, the Hecke transform
{\cal T}_{n}\chi(\tau)
has positive integer Fourier coefficients
n\,b_{m}^{(n)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
as well; c.f. the discussion after (
10.4.4
). The application of Hecke operators
{\cal T}_{n}
to a CFT character thus produces another function, which itself has some properties expected of a CFT character. In this sense, we might hope that the Hecke operators provide a “map between CFTs,” as in the example of the Ising and Baby Monster CFTs mentioned above.
We now study this phenomenon in the simplest examples of holomorphically factorizable CFTs, i.e. those for which the partition function is given simply as a product
Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=Z(\tau)\overline{Z(\tau)}
.

Let us consider a class of two-dimensional CFTs arising as the holographic duals to three-dimensional gravity in anti-de Sitter space AdS
3
. We will consider theories of pure gravity, which in three dimensions are known to not have any propagating gravitational waves. Indeed, three-dimensional gravity can be rewritten as a topological theory, namely an
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
Chern-Simons theory specified by levels
k_{L},k_{R}
. Here we work with the case of
k_{L}=k_{R}=\kappa
. By comparing the Chern-Simons formulation of the theory with the Einstein-Hilbert formulation, one can show that the curvature radius of AdS
3
is related to the level
\kappa
by
\ell=16\kappa G_{N}
, where
G_{N}
is Newton’s constant. In particular, we see that
\ell
is quantized.

What do the tentative dual CFTs look like? By the Brown-Henneaux formula
[
129
]
, the CFTs should have central charges,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}={3\ell\over 2G_{N}}=24\kappa

(10.10.9)

The vacuum energy is given by
-{\mathfrak{c}\over 24}=-\kappa
. Additional states can be obtained from the vacuum state by application of the generators of the Virasoro algebra. In particular, the generators
L_{n}
for
n\geq-1
annihilate the vacuum, while the generators
L_{-n}
for
n>1
act on the vacuum to give new states with energy
-\kappa+n
. For given energy
-\kappa+n
, the naive degeneracy would be given by
p(n)
, the number of integer partitions of
n
. For example, for
n=3
we have
p(3)=3
, corresponding to the three states,

\displaystyle L_{-1}^{3}|0\rangle\hskip 43.36243ptL_{-1}L_{-2}|0\rangle\hskip 43.36243ptL_{-3}|0\rangle

(10.10.10)

This would give total vacuum contribution to the chiral partition function,

\displaystyle Z_{\kappa}^{\rm vac}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}q^{-\kappa}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p(n)q^{n}=q^{-\kappa}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over 1-q^{n}}

(10.10.11)

as per the discussion in section
5.6.2
. However, in the current case
L_{-1}
annihilates the vacuum, and the corresponding degeneracies must be removed. This is accomplished by including a factor of
(1-q)
, giving the actual vacuum contribution

\displaystyle Z_{\kappa}^{\rm vac}(\tau)=q^{-\kappa}\prod_{n=2}^{\infty}{1\over 1-q^{n}}

(10.10.12)

The vacuum contribution to the partition function is not itself modular invariant, which means that there must be additional states beyond the vacuum and its descendants. Indeed, there must be some states in the CFT which capture the known black holes states of the holographic dual. These black holes are expected on general grounds to appear at order
O(q^{1})
, giving a full partition function of the form

\displaystyle Z_{\kappa}(\tau)=q^{-\kappa}\prod_{n=2}^{\infty}{1\over 1-q^{n}}+{\cal O}(q)

(10.10.13)

Remarkably, there is a unique such partition function for every
\kappa
.

For
\kappa=1
, i.e. pure AdS
3
gravity with the smallest value of the cosmological constant, the only possible candidate is

\displaystyle Z_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j(\tau)-744

(10.10.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-1}+196884\,q+\dots

with
j(\tau)
the
j
-function introduced in section
3.7
. Physically, the 196884 states at level 1 can be interpreted as 1 descendant of the vacuum, and an additional 196883 black hole states. The 2d CFT with this partition function was first constructed by Frenkel, Lepowsky, and Merman and is known as the “Monster CFT,” due to it having global symmetry group given by the Fischer-Greiss monster
\mathbb{M}
, the largest finite sporadic group. The smallest non-trivial representation of
\mathbb{M}
has dimension 196883, and the black hole states indeed lie in this representation.

To obtain partition functions for higher
\kappa>1
, we now make use of Hecke operators. Thus far in this section we have focused on Hecke operators acting on holomorphic modular forms, but the extension to the meromorphic case is straightforward. The
q
-expansions of Hecke transforms can be obtained using (
10.4.3
), giving e.g.

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{2}J(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-2}+42987520\,q+\dots

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{3}J(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-3}+2592899910\,q+\dots

(10.10.15)

where we have defined
J(\tau)=j(\tau)-744
. For any
\kappa
, it is easy to show that one has

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{\kappa}J(\tau)=q^{-\kappa}+{\cal O}(q)

(10.10.16)

Using this, we can now write down closed form expressions for
Z_{\kappa}(\tau)
as follows. Say that

\displaystyle Z_{\kappa}^{\rm vac}(\tau)=\sum_{r=-\kappa}^{\infty}c_{r}q^{r}

(10.10.17)

Of course, using (
10.10.12
) all
c_{r}
are known. Then, the full chiral partition function is given simply by

\displaystyle Z_{\kappa}(\tau)=\sum_{r=0}^{\kappa}c_{-r}{\cal T}_{r}J

(10.10.18)

where we take
{\cal T}_{0}J(\tau)=1
. Using the formulas above, we get explicitly

\displaystyle Z_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-2}+1+42987520\,q+\dots

\displaystyle Z_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-3}+q^{-1}+1+2593096794\,q+\dots

(10.10.19)

and so on. Though no concrete construction of CFTs with these partition functions are known (assuming they actually exist), there exists much literature on the subject. Such tentative theories are referred to as “extremal CFTs.” We have thus seen that the Hecke operators act as maps between extremal CFTs.

Furthermore, by using Hecke operators we have obtained the exact number of quantum black hole microstates in theories of pure gravity. In particular, this number is obtained by subtracting the number of vacuum descendants from the coefficient of the
{\cal O}(q^{1})
term. It is interesting to compare these results to those predicted by the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy,

\displaystyle S_{BH}=4\pi\sqrt{\kappa L_{0}}

(10.10.20)

For example, for
\kappa=L_{0}=1
one finds
S_{BH}=4\pi\approx 12.57
, to be compared to the exact result
S_{\rm exact}=\log 196883\approx 12.19
. The agreement between the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy and the exact microstate count improves in the semiclassical limit
\kappa,L_{0}\rightarrow\infty
with
L_{0}/k
fixed. The origin of this agreement is in fact a classic result of Petersson and Rademacher. Writing the Fourier expansion of
j(\tau)
as

\displaystyle j(\tau)-744=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}c_{n}q^{n}

(10.10.21)

with
c_{0}=0
, Petersson and Rademacher proved that as
n\rightarrow\infty
, one has

\displaystyle\log c_{n}\approx 4\pi\sqrt{n}-{3\over 4}\ln m-{1\over 2}\ln 2+\dots

(10.10.22)

We now combine this with the results for the Fourier coefficients of Hecke transforms. Since we are working in the limit
\kappa,L_{0}\rightarrow\infty
, it suffices to consider only the
{\cal T}_{\kappa}J
contribution to (
10.10.18
). The dominant contribution to the corresponding Fourier coefficients
b^{(\kappa)}_{n}
are
b^{(\kappa)}_{n}\approx\kappa c_{\kappa n}
, which gives

\displaystyle\log b^{(\kappa)}_{n}\approx 4\pi\sqrt{\kappa n}+{1\over 4}\ln\kappa-{3\over 4}\ln n-{1\over 2}\ln 2+\dots

(10.10.23)

The first term reproduces the Bekenstein-Hawking result, while the others give the leading-order quantum corrections.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The formal theory of Hecke operators was developed by Hecke in
[
130
,
131
]
, but the operators had been introduced two decades earlier when Mordell proved the multiplication law of the Ramanujan
\tau
-function
[
132
]
, reproduced in section
10.5
. Most of the material discussed above is by now standard, and may be found in the books by Apostol
[
8
]
, Shimura
[
11
]
, Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
, and Serre
[
128
]
. The theory of Hecke operators acting on Maass forms is described in the book by Terras
[
20
]
. The double coset construction is described in detail in the book by Diamond and Shurman
[
13
]
and the book by Iwaniec
[
15
]
. The action of Hecke operators on vector-valued modular forms was developed recently in both physics
[
133
,
134
]
and mathematics
[
135
]
literature.

The discussion of three-dimensional gravity given in section
10.10
follows closely the work of
[
136
]
. For standard introductions to two-dimensional quantum field theory and conformal field theory we refer again to
[
48
,
47
]
, already mentioned in section
5
. The basic tools of AdS/CFT are reviewed in
[
137
,
138
]
as well as in the book by Ammon and Erdmenger
[
139
]
. The non-existence of several extremal CFTs was recently proven in
[
140
]
, using the theory of topological modular forms. The asymptotic form of the coefficients of the
j
-function quoted in (
10.10.22
) was obtained by Petersson in
[
141
]
, and independently by Rademacher
[
142
]
.

11

Singular moduli and complex multiplication

In previous sections we introduced
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
as the automorphism group for any lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{1}+{\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{2}
with
{\rm Im\,}(\omega_{2}/\omega_{1})>0
. Singular moduli correspond to special values of
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
for which the lattice has an automorphism group that is larger than
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The mechanism by which this happens is referred to as complex multiplication.

A first rather obvious example is when
\tau=i
, in which case the lattice has an extra symmetry group
\{\pm I,\pm S\}
of order four. A second obvious example is when
\tau=\rho=e^{2\pi i/3}
, in which case the extra symmetry group
\{\pm I,\pm(ST),\pm(ST)^{2}\}
is of order six. One way of looking at these automorphisms is by complex multiplication, namely in the first case we may take the periods to be
\omega_{1}=1
and
\omega_{2}=i
, and the extra non-trivial symmetries correspond mapping
\omega_{1}\to i\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{2}\to i\omega_{2}=-\omega_{1}
, leaving the lattice invariant.

11.1

Conditions for complex multiplication

We seek the lattices for which there exists a complex number
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\alpha\Lambda\subset\Lambda
. The condition may be written out explicitly on the periods as follows,

\displaystyle\alpha\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\omega_{2}+b\omega_{1}

\displaystyle\alpha\omega_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\omega_{2}+d\omega_{1}

(11.1.1)

with
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
ad-bc\not=0
. Note that we are not requiring
ad-bc=1
since we only demand that
\alpha\Lambda
be contained in
\Lambda
without necessarily being equal to
\Lambda
. Taking the ratio gives a condition on
\tau
, while given such a solution
\tau
, the value of
\alpha
is then determined,

\displaystyle\tau={a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\hskip 72.26999pt\alpha=c\tau+d

(11.1.2)

The condition on
\tau
is that it satisfies a quadratic polynomial equation,

\displaystyle c\tau^{2}+(d-a)\tau-b=0

(11.1.3)

with integer coefficients. The discriminant of this equation is denoted by
D
,

\displaystyle D=(a-d)^{2}+4bc=(a+d)^{2}-4(ad-bc)

(11.1.4)

The restriction
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)\not=0
imposes the condition
D<0
, which in turn requires
bc<0
and
ad-bc\geq 1
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
c>0
and define
\sqrt{D}
to be the square root of
D
satisfying
{\rm Im\,}(\sqrt{D})>0
, so that we have,

\displaystyle\tau={a-d+\sqrt{D}\over 2c}\hskip 72.26999pt\alpha={a+d+\sqrt{D}\over 2}\hskip 57.81621pt|\alpha|^{2}=ad-bc

(11.1.5)

Extending the field of the rationals
{\mathbb{Q}}
by
\tau
gives the quadratic extension field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\tau)
.

11.2

Elliptic functions at complex multiplication points

When
\alpha\Lambda\subset\Lambda
and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}
, elliptic functions and modular forms at
\tau
satisfy remarkable properties. A first key result is for elliptic functions. It suffices to investigate the Weierstrass function
\wp(z)=\wp(z|\Lambda)
for the lattice
\Lambda
. Since
\alpha\Lambda\subset\Lambda
, the function
\wp(\alpha z)
is an elliptic function for the original lattice
\Lambda
and may therefore be expressed as a rational function of
\wp(z)
. More specifically, we have,

\displaystyle\wp(\alpha z)={A(\wp(z))\over B(\wp(z))}

(11.2.1)

with
A
and
B
relatively prime polynomials of degrees related by,

\displaystyle\deg A=1+\deg B=|\alpha|^{2}=ad-bc

(11.2.2)

The only assertion above that needs proof is the relation between the degrees. First, since
\wp(\alpha z)
has a double pole at
z=0
and since
A
and
B
will have poles at
z=0
of respective degrees
2\deg A
and
2\deg B
, we must have the first equality above between the degrees. Second, the lattice
\alpha\Lambda
has a fundamental parallelogram which is scaled up from
\Lambda
by a factor of
|\alpha|
, so that the area of the fundamental parallelogram is
|\alpha|^{2}
times that of the fundamental parallelogram of
\Lambda
. Thus, the index is given by
[\Lambda:\alpha\Lambda]=|\alpha|^{2}
. While
\wp(z)
has one double pole in the fundamental parallelogram of
\Lambda
, the total number of poles of
\wp(\alpha z)
in the fundamental parallelogram of
\Lambda
is
2|\alpha|^{2}
, which is also its number of zeros. It follows that
\deg A=|\alpha|^{2}
giving the second equality.

Constructing the polynomials
A,B
is achieved by matching the Laurent expansions of both sides at
z=0
using the Laurent series of
\wp(z)
,

\displaystyle\wp(z)={1\over z^{2}}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(2m+1)G_{2m+2}z^{2m}

(11.2.3)

where we have taken into account the fact that
G_{m}=0
for
m
odd. We shall also use the fact that
G_{2m}
for
m\geq 4
may be expressed as a polynomial in the generators
G_{4},G_{6}
of the ring of modular forms of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of (
3.5.22
),

\displaystyle G_{2m+4}=\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}{3(2k+1)(2m-2k+1)\over(m-1)(2m+3)(2m+5)}\,G_{2k+2}G_{2m-2k+2}

(11.2.4)

Of course, the relation between
\wp(\alpha z)
and
\wp(z)
, and therefore the matching of the Laurent series, will only be possible at the singular modulus
\tau
for which we have the corresponding complex conjugation factor
\alpha
. Therefore, the matching will impose a relation between the Eisenstein series
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
, and this will give a formula for the value of
j(\tau)
.

11.3

Examples

Consider the simple example
\alpha=i\sqrt{2}
for which
a+d=0
, the discriminant is given by
D=-8
, and
|\alpha|^{2}=2
. We may take the representative
\tau=i\sqrt{2}
. In this case
A
has degree two and
B
degree one, so that we may express the relation (
11.2.1
) as,

\displaystyle\wp(i\sqrt{2}\,z)=c_{1}\wp(z)+c_{2}+{c_{3}\over\wp(z)+c_{4}}

(11.3.1)

Since
\wp(i\sqrt{2}\,z)=-1/(2z^{2})+{\cal O}(z^{2})
we must have
c_{1}=-{1\over 2}
and
c_{2}=0
.
Substituting the Laurent expansion we obtain the following expression,

\displaystyle\left(\wp(i\sqrt{2}\,z)+{1\over 2}\wp(z)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{2\over 9G_{4}}\left({1\over z^{2}}+5{G_{6}\over G_{4}}+25{G_{6}^{2}\over G_{4}^{2}}z^{2}-5G_{4}z^{2}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\right)

(11.3.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over c_{3}}\left(\wp(z)+c_{4}+25{G_{6}^{2}\over G_{4}^{2}}z^{2}-8G_{4}z^{2}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\right)

To match the Laurent expansions we have set,

\displaystyle c_{3}=-{9\over 2}G_{4}\hskip 72.26999ptc_{4}=5{G_{6}\over G_{4}}

(11.3.3)

But the relation must be exact to all orders in
z
by our global analysis, so the terms of order
z^{2}
and higher in the above parentheses must vanish. This can be achieved only at the singular modulus associated with the complex multiplication factor
\alpha
, and this gives us a relation between
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
,

\displaystyle 25\,G_{6}^{2}=8G_{4}^{3}

(11.3.4)

It may be checked that all higher order terms in the parentheses on the second line also vanish. We thus find that the
j
-invariant at the complex multiplication point is given by,

\displaystyle j(i\sqrt{2})={(12)^{3}\,g_{2}^{3}\over g_{2}^{3}-27g_{3}^{2}}=(12)^{3}\left(1-{49\,G_{6}^{2}\over 20G_{4}^{3}}\right)^{-1}=(20)^{3}=8000

(11.3.5)

Remarkably,
j(i\sqrt{2})
is an integer, and in fact the cube of an integer !

11.3.1

Heegner points

A related set of points are those labelled by the so-called Heegner numbers. The Heegner numbers can be defined as numbers
d
such that
j\left({1\over 2}(1+i\sqrt{d})\right)\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. There exist precisely eight such numbers,

\displaystyle 1,\,3,\,7,\,11,\,19,\,43,\,67,\,163

(11.3.6)

a fact first conjectured by Gauss and proven by Heegner, Baker, and Stark over a century later. It is a remarkable fact that the
j
-invariant evaluates to a cube on each of them,

\displaystyle j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12^{3}\hskip 57.81621ptj\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{3})\right)=0

\displaystyle j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{7})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-15^{3}\hskip 46.97505ptj\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{11})\right)=-32^{3}

\displaystyle j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{19})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-96^{3}\hskip 46.97505ptj\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{43})\right)=-960^{3}

\displaystyle j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{67})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-5280^{3}\hskip 36.135ptj\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{163})\right)=-640320^{3}

(11.3.7)

These are precisely the discriminants
D=-d
for which the class number is one and the corresponding ring of integers has unique factorization.

Incidentally, the Heegner numbers are related to some well-known examples of “almost integers.” Perhaps the most famous almost integer is Ramanujan’s constant,

\displaystyle e^{\pi\sqrt{163}}=262537412640768743.99999999999925\dots

(11.3.8)

whose near-integrality can be explained by using the value of
j\left({1\over 2}(1+i\sqrt{163})\right)
given above together with the
q
-expansion of
j(\tau)
to write

\displaystyle e^{\pi\sqrt{163}}=640320^{3}+744+O(e^{-\pi\sqrt{163}})

(11.3.9)

11.4

j(\tau)
as an algebraic integer

An algebraic integer is a complex number that is a root of a monic polynomial with integer coefficients, namely of a monic polynomial in
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
. Recall that a monic polynomial is one whose leading monomial has coefficient 1. A general result regarding the arithmetic properties of
j(\tau)
at points
\tau
of complex multiplication is the following:

Theorem 11.1

For a lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}+\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
with complex multiplication,
j(\tau)
is an algebraic integer.

To prove this result, we define the following monic polynomial in
x
for given
\tau
,

\displaystyle P_{n}(x|\tau)=\prod_{\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}}\left(x-j(\gamma\tau)\right)

(11.4.1)

where
M_{n}
is the set of
2\times 2
matrices with determinant
n
, introduced in (
10.1.3
). Since
j(\tau)
is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
the product in
\gamma
is taken over the left cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
discussed above (
10.1.4
). The function
P_{n}(x|\tau)
is invariant under
\tau\to\gamma\tau
for all
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
because the right action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on the left cosets
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
simply permutes the cosets.

Because the set of matrices,

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\big|\,ad=n,\,0\leq b\leq d-1\right\}

(11.4.2)

provides a complete set of representatives of the quotient
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
, as was explained in section
10.1
, we obtain the following equivalent product formula,

\displaystyle P_{n}(x|\tau)=\prod_{ad=n}~\prod_{b=0}^{d-1}\left(x-j\left({a\tau+b\over d}\right)\right)

(11.4.3)

This expression confirms that
P_{n}(x|\tau+1)=P_{n}(x|\tau)
since the shift in
\tau
amounts to a shift
b\to b+a~({\rm mod~}d)
under which the product over
b
is invariant. The degree of
P_{n}
is given by,

\displaystyle\deg(P_{n})=\#(SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n})=\sigma_{1}(n)

(11.4.4)

where
\sigma_{1}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d
is the divisor sum.
The full structure of the
\tau
-dependence of
P_{n}(x|\tau)
is given by the following Lemma.

Lemma 11.2

P_{n}(x|\tau)
is a polynomial in
x
and
j(\tau)
with integer coefficients, so that there exists a polynomial
Q_{n}(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x,y]
which satisfies
P_{n}(x|\tau)=Q_{n}(x,j(\tau))
.

To prove the Lemma, we begin by focussing on the product over
b
for given
d
. Writing the
q
-expansion of
j(\tau)
as
j(\tau)=q^{-1}+744+\sum_{k\geq 1}c_{k}q^{k}
with
c_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have,

\displaystyle\prod_{0\leq b\leq d-1}\left(x-j\left(\tfrac{a\tau+b}{d}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d}\prod_{0\leq b\leq d-1}\left(e^{-{2\pi ib\over d}}q^{-{a\over d}}+744+\sum_{k\geq 1}c_{k}e^{{2\pi ikb\over d}}q^{ka\over d}-x\right)

(11.4.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d}q^{-a}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1-d}a_{\ell}(x)q^{\ell a\over d}

By construction, the coefficients
a_{\ell}(x)
are polynomials in
x
and the root of unity
e^{2\pi i/d}
, namely
a_{\ell}(x)\in{\mathbb{Z}}(x,e^{2\pi i\over d})
. Actually, (
11.4.5
) implies that the
a_{\ell}(x)
are invariant under all transformations
\sigma_{r}:e^{2\pi i\over d}\rightarrow e^{2\pi ir\over d}
for
0\leq r\leq d-1
; that is, the so-called “Galois automorphisms” of
{\mathbb{Q}}(e^{2\pi i\over d})/{\mathbb{Q}}
, which will be further discussed in section
16
. As a result,
a_{\ell}(x)
must be independent of
e^{2\pi i\over d}
and therefore belongs to
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
. Furthermore, the left side of (
11.4.5
) is invariant under
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
, so that we must have
a_{\ell}(x)=0
for all
d\nmid\ell
. The function
P_{n}(x|\tau)
is obtained by taking the product of (
11.4.5
) over
ad=n
, which we
reorganize as follows,

\displaystyle P_{n}(x|\tau)=\prod_{ad=n}\left[(-1)^{d}q^{-a}+\sum_{m\geq 0}a_{md}(x)q^{ma}\right]=\sum_{r=0}^{\deg(P_{n})}h_{r}(\tau)x^{r}

(11.4.6)

where the coefficient functions
h_{r}(\tau)
admit a Fourier expansion
h_{r}(\tau)=\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell}^{(r)}q^{\ell}
with
a_{\ell}^{(r)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Recall that any meromorphic modular function with integer Fourier coefficients can be written as a polynomial in
j(\tau)
with integer coefficients; that is, we can write
h_{r}(\tau)=\sum_{\ell}\tilde{a}_{\ell}^{(r)}j(\tau)^{\ell}
with
\tilde{a}_{\ell}^{(r)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. This allows us to conclude that the same is true of
P_{n}(x|\tau)
, thereby proving that
P_{n}(x|\tau)
is a polynomial in
x
and
j(\tau)
with integer coefficient. This result may be summarized by the statement that there exists a polynomial
Q_{n}(x,y)
with integer coefficients such that,

\displaystyle P_{n}(x|\tau)=Q_{n}(x,j(\tau))

(11.4.7)

Actually
Q_{n}
satisfies
Q_{n}(y,x)=\pm Q_{n}(x,y)
and is of degree
\sigma_{1}(n)
in
x
and thus also in
y
.

To complete the proof of Theorem
11.1
, we now specialize to a point
\tau=\tau_{\rm CM}\in{\cal H}
of complex multiplication of order
n
, namely satisfying
\gamma_{0}\tau_{\rm CM}=\tau_{\rm CM}
for a matrix
\gamma_{0}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{n}
with determinant
n=ad-bc
. The polynomial
P_{n}(x|\tau_{{\rm CM}})=Q_{n}(x,j(\tau_{{\rm CM}})
must vanish at the point
x=j(\tau_{{\rm CM}})
since, up to an
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformation,
\gamma_{0}
will coincide with one of the
\gamma
-matrices in the product in (
11.4.1
). Thus, the polynomial
Q_{n}(x,x)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
with integer coefficients vanishes at
x=j(\tau_{{\rm CM}})
.

To complete the proof that
j(\tau_{{\rm CM}})
is an algebraic integer, it remains to show that it is a root of a non-vanishing polynomial with integer coefficients. To this end, we evaluate the leading behavior at the cusp of the polynomial
Q_{n}(x,x)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
from (
11.4.3
) with the help of the factorization formula,

\displaystyle\prod_{b=0}^{d-1}(x-e^{2\pi ib/d}y)=x^{d}-y^{d}

(11.4.8)

and we obtain,

\displaystyle Q_{n}(j(\tau),j(\tau))=\prod_{ad=n}\Big(q^{-d}-q^{-a}+\hbox{ lower order terms }\Big)

(11.4.9)

There are now two cases to be considered, depending on whether
n
is a perfect square or not. We first consider the case when
n
is not a perfect square. Every factor in the product has
a\not=d
since otherwise
n=ad=a^{2}
is a perfect square and the leading behavior at the cusp is given by the power of
q
obtained by summing the maxima of
(d,a)
,

\displaystyle Q_{n}(j(\tau),j(\tau))=\pm q^{-\sigma_{1}^{+}(n)}+\hbox{ lower orders }\hskip 43.36243pt\sigma_{1}^{+}(n)=\sum_{ad=n}\max(a,d)

(11.4.10)

Therefore, the polynomial
Q_{n}(x,x)
is non-vanishing and monic (up to a sign) when
n
is not a perfect square.
When
n=\nu^{2}
is a perfect square, then one element in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash M_{n}
is of the form
\nu I
so that
a=d=\nu
and
Q_{n}(j(\tau),j(\tau))=0
. The vanishing is caused by the fact that
Q_{n}(x,y)
has a factor
(x-y)
. Adapting the above arguments to this case, one shows that the polynomial,

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{n}(x,y)={Q_{n}(x,y)\over(x-y)}

(11.4.11)

evaluated at
x=y=j(\tau)
is an integer multiple of a monic polynomial of degree
\sigma_{1}^{+}(n)-n
. This completes the proof of the Theorem.

11.4.1

The examples of
n=2,3,4,5

Zagier worked out the example of
n=2
in detail. The cases
n=3,4,5
may be obtained analogously, and we find the following factorizations of
Q_{n}(x,x)
,

\displaystyle Q_{2}(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(x-12^{3})(x-20^{3})(x+15^{3})^{2}

(11.4.12)

\displaystyle Q_{3}(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-x(x-20^{3})(x+32^{3})(x-54000)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{4}(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(x+15^{3})^{2}(x-54000)^{2}(x-66^{3})(x^{2}+191025x-121287375)^{2}

\displaystyle Q_{5}(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x-12^{3})^{2}(x+32^{3})^{2}(x-66^{3})^{2}(x+96^{3})^{2}(x^{2}-1264000x-681472000)

The second order polynomials factor as follows,

\displaystyle x^{2}+191025x-121287375

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-191025+85995\sqrt{5}))(x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-191025-85995\sqrt{5}))

\displaystyle x^{2}-1264000x-681472000

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x-632000-282889\sqrt{5})(x-632000+282889\sqrt{5})

(11.4.13)

In addition to the entries listed in (
11.3.1
) we also recognize,

\displaystyle j(i)

\displaystyle=12^{3}

\displaystyle j(i\sqrt{3})

\displaystyle=54000

\displaystyle j(2i)

\displaystyle=66^{3}

\displaystyle j(i\sqrt{5})

\displaystyle=632000+282880\sqrt{5}

\displaystyle j(i\sqrt{2})

\displaystyle=20^{3}

\displaystyle j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+i\sqrt{15})\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(191025+85995\sqrt{5}\big)

(11.4.14)

11.5

\vartheta
-functions at complex multiplication points

We now examine Jacobi theta functions at some arguments
\tau=i\sqrt{n}
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
To do so, we begin by defining the function,

\displaystyle K(k)\,\,=\,\,\int_{0}^{\pi\over 2}{dx\over\sqrt{1-k^{2}\sin^{2}x}}\,\,=\,\,{\pi\over 2}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left({1\over 2},{1\over 2};1;k^{2}\right)

(11.5.1)

known as a complete elliptic integral of the first kind. A valuable result involving elliptic integrals and Jacobi theta-functions is that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)=\sqrt{{2\over\pi}K(k)}

(11.5.2)

where the parameter
\tau
is defined in terms of the elliptic integrals as,

\displaystyle\tau=iF(k)~,\hskip 36.135ptF(k)={K(\sqrt{1-k^{2}})\over K(k)}=\,{{}_{2}F_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;1-k^{2})\over{}_{2}F_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;k^{2})}

(11.5.3)

In this presentation, the action of the generators
T
and
S
of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
corresponds to,

\displaystyle T:\hskip 36.135pt\tau

\displaystyle\to\tau+1

\displaystyle k^{2}

\displaystyle\to-{k^{2}\over 1-k^{2}}

\displaystyle S:\hskip 36.135pt\tau

\displaystyle\to-{1\over\tau}

\displaystyle k^{2}

\displaystyle\to 1-k^{2}

(11.5.4)

which may be readily verified using the transformation formulas for the hypergeometric function
(see Bateman
[
1
]
, Vol II page 318).

As we will now describe, the above relations between
\vartheta
-functions and hypergeometric functions can be used to evaluate
\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)
at special values of
\tau
. Having obtained
\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)
, one can then obtain the values of other theta functions by means of,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(0|\tau)=\sqrt{k}\,\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)\hskip 36.135pt\vartheta_{4}(0|\tau)=(1-k^{2})^{1/4}\,\vartheta_{3}(0|\tau)

(11.5.5)

11.5.1

The point
\tau=i

Let us begin by evaluating
\vartheta_{3}(0|i)
. In this case, (
11.5.3
) tells us to consider
F(k)=1
, i.e.

\displaystyle K(\sqrt{1-k^{2}})=K(k)

(11.5.6)

This is solved by
k={1\over\sqrt{2}}
. Using this in (
11.5.2
) then gives

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|i)=\sqrt{{2\over\pi}K\left({1\over\sqrt{2}}\right)}

(11.5.7)

In order to evaluate the remaining elliptic integral, we convert it to a hypergeometric function as per the second equality of (
11.5.1
) and make use of the following standard result,

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(a,b;{1\over 2}(a+b+1);{1\over 2}\right)={\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma({1\over 2}(a+b+1))\over\Gamma({1\over 2}(1+a))\Gamma({1\over 2}(1+b))}

(11.5.8)

valid for arbitrary
a,b
. Using this, we conclude that,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|i)=\frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\Gamma(\tfrac{3}{4})}

(11.5.9)

11.5.2

The point
\tau=2i

Next consider
\vartheta_{3}(0|2i)
. We begin by searching for
k
such that
F(k)=2
. To do so, we make use of the elliptic integral identity,

\displaystyle F(k)=2F\left({2\sqrt{k}\over 1+k}\right)

(11.5.10)

which follows from a similar functional identity involving hypergeometric functions,
(see for example Bateman
[
143
]
, Vol I, page 110)

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\frac{4x}{(1+x)^{2}}\right)=(1+x)\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;x^{2}\right)

(11.5.11)

Using this identity, we may recast our equation for
k
as follows,

\displaystyle F\left({2\sqrt{k}\over 1+k}\right)=1

(11.5.12)

The solution to this equation was identified in the previous subsection—we require,

\displaystyle{2\sqrt{k}\over 1+k}={1\over\sqrt{2}}\hskip 36.135pt\Rightarrow\hskip 36.135ptk=(\sqrt{2}-1)^{2}

(11.5.13)

and thus we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|2i)=\sqrt{{}_{2}F_{1}\left({1\over 2},{1\over 2};1;(\sqrt{2}-1)^{4}\right)}

(11.5.14)

Making use of duplication and reflection identities of hypergeometric functions, one shows,

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;(\sqrt{2}-1)^{4}\right)=\frac{1}{4-2\sqrt{2}}\,\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\tfrac{1}{2}\right)

(11.5.15)

and then using (
11.5.8
) we obtain,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|2i)=\frac{\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}}{2\Gamma(\tfrac{3}{4})}\sqrt{2+\sqrt{2}}

(11.5.16)

11.5.3

The point
\tau=\sqrt{2}\,i

Finally we consider
\vartheta_{3}(0|\sqrt{2}i)
. We would like to solve
F(k)=\sqrt{2}
, or equivalently,

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;k^{2}\right)={}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;1-k^{2}\right)

(11.5.17)

Comparing this to (
11.5.11
), we see that
k=\sqrt{2}-1
is a solution. Thus we have,

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(0|\sqrt{2}i)=\sqrt{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;(\sqrt{2}-1)^{2}\right)}

(11.5.18)

To proceed, we again use duplication and reflection identities of hypergeometric functions to show that,

\displaystyle{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;(\sqrt{2}-1)^{2}\right)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4-2\sqrt{2}}}\,\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{4},\tfrac{3}{4};1;\tfrac{1}{2}\right)

(11.5.19)

from which we can evaluate,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|\sqrt{2}i)=\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{9}{8})}{\Gamma(\tfrac{5}{4})}\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{4})}{2^{1/4}\pi}}

(11.5.20)

11.5.4

The points
\tau=in
and
\tau=i/n
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

To close, we quote without proof the results for some other
\vartheta
-values at imaginary complex multiplication points
\vartheta_{3}(0|in)
. It is a general fact that one can write,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(0|in)=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(0|i)}{n^{1/4}h_{n}}

(11.5.21)

with
\vartheta_{3}(0|i)
given in (
11.5.9
). The first few values of
h_{n}
are found to be,

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\sqrt{2}-2}

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\sqrt{3}-3)^{1/4}

\displaystyle h_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}+1}

\displaystyle h_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{5-2\sqrt{5}}

\displaystyle h_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{3/4}3^{1/8}((\sqrt{2}-1)(\sqrt{3}-1))^{1/6}}{(-4+3\sqrt{2}+3^{5/4}+2\sqrt{3}-3^{3/4}+2^{3/2}3^{3/4})^{1/3}}

(11.5.22)

From the above results, one can also obtain expressions for
\vartheta_{3}(0|{i/n})
by means of the following useful relation,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}\left(0|{i/\sqrt{n}}\right)=n^{1/4}\,\vartheta_{3}(0|i\sqrt{n})

(11.5.23)

11.6

The values of
{\sf E}_{2},\,{\sf E}_{4},\,{\sf E}_{6}
at the points
\tau=i,\rho

The quasi-modular transformation property of
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{2}{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+{12\over 2\pi i}\,c(c\tau+d)

(11.6.1)

allows one to evaluate
{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)
at the points
\tau=i
and
\tau=\rho=e^{2\pi i/3}
. These points are fixed points of the modular transformations
\gamma=S
, for which
(c,d)=(1,0)
, and
\gamma=ST
, for which
(c,d)=(1,1)
, respectively. Substituting these expressions into the transformation law (
11.6.1
), we readily obtain,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2}(i)={3\over\pi}\hskip 72.26999pt{\sf E}_{2}(\rho)={2\sqrt{3}\over\pi}

(11.6.2)

The modular forms
{\sf E}_{4}
and
{\sf E}_{6}
vanish at the points
\rho
and
i
respectively, as was already shown in (
3.6.4
) in terms of the closely related modular forms
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
. Evaluating
{\sf E}_{4}
and
{\sf E}_{6}
at the points
i
and
\rho
respectively requires expressing elliptic integrals in terms of hypergeometric functions, and we shall quote here the result without proof,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{4}(i)

\displaystyle={48\,\Gamma(\tfrac{5}{4})^{4}\over\pi^{2}\,\Gamma(\tfrac{3}{4})^{4}}

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{4}(\rho)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{6}(i)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{6}(\rho)

\displaystyle={729\,\Gamma(\tfrac{4}{3})^{6}\over 2\pi^{3}\,\Gamma(\tfrac{5}{6})^{6}}

(11.6.3)

For a derivation of the first and fourth relations, see Diamond and Shurman
[

]
.

[

Generally speaking, points of enhanced symmetry tend to play a special role in physics, and singular moduli are precisely the values of the moduli where such enhancements occur. As such, singular moduli and complex multiplication have appeared in various places in physics. Concrete examples can be found in Seiberg-Witten theory
[
149
,
150
,
151
,
152
,
153
]
, where points on the Coulomb branch corresponding to singular moduli generically have extra massless degrees of freedom, as well as in string theory where the additional discrete symmetries at singular values of the axion-dilaton can be gauged to give so-called “S-folds”
[
154
,
155
,
156
,
157
,
158
]
. In section
13.6
we will introduce another physical application: namely we will show that a non-linear sigma model CFT with
T^{2}
target space is rational if and only if the torus admits complex multiplication
[
159
]
.

We close by noting that another context in which singular moduli appear is in the study of orbifolds, in which one quotients by the emergent symmetry at such points. In the context of perturbative string theory on a target space torus, orbifolds were first introduced in
[
160
,
161
]
, and were further developed in
[
162
]
among many others publications. A large number of references (with a particular emphasis on string phenomenology) can be found in the bibliography of
[

. The study of singular moduli at genus-2 was carried out in the 1960s by Gottschling in

[

Some more general comments at arbitrary genus, from a two-dimensional conformal field theory perspective, can be found in
