.

12

String amplitudes

We now finally turn to the topic of string theory. The starting point for string theory is the assumption that elementary particles are one-dimensional objects, namely strings, in contrast to quantum field theory where elementary particles are assumed to be point-like. The size of strings is set by the Planck length
\ell_{P}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{P}=\sqrt{G_{N}\hbar/c^{3}}\approx 10^{-33}\,{\rm cm}

(12.0.1)

where
G_{N}
is Newton’s gravitational constant,
\hbar
is Planck’s constant, and
c
is the speed of light.
The Planck length is approximately
10^{-19}
times the size of a proton. All distance scales accessible to us to date are enormously larger than
\ell_{P}
. At long distance scales or, equivalently, at low energy scales strings effectively behave as point-like particles.

12.1

Overview

Being a one-dimensional object, a connected string may have two different topologies: that of a circle or closed string, or that of an interval or open string. The time evolution of a closed string sweeps out a two-dimensional surface with the topology of a cylinder, represented in the left panel of Figure
10
, while an open string sweeps out a rectangle. The surface swept out by a string is referred to as the worldsheet. One of the most striking features of string theory is that all interactions are governed by the joining and splitting of strings, as represented in the right panel of Figure
10
for closed strings, without the need for the point-like interactions required in quantum field theory. The interaction by joining and splitting is unique once the free propagation of the string is known, since locally on the worldsheet there is no way to distinguish free propagation from interaction.

Figure 10
:
Time evolution of a free closed string is represented in the left panel. Interaction by joining and splitting of closed strings is represented in the right panel.

String theory may be promoted into a consistent quantum theory provided certain conditions are met on the space-time in which strings propagate and interact. Superstrings can propagate in flat space-time provided the dimension of the space-time is ten. The spectrum of superstring theory then automatically contains a graviton, and string theory is automatically a theory of quantum gravity, albeit in space-time dimension greater than four. Supersymmetry, which swaps boson and fermion states, is a key ingredient in making superstring theory mathematically and physically consistent. The center of attention in these lectures is modular invariance which is responsible for making superstring theory UV finite.

The ultimate goal of string theory is the unification of gravity with the Standard Model of Particle Physics into a single theory that is consistent with quantum mechanics and general relativity. Successful unification will require principles and mechanisms for selecting our four-dimensional universe from all the solutions of string theory, for breaking space-time supersymmetry, and for maintaining a small cosmological constant in the process. Satisfactory answers to these questions remain largely outstanding today.

In this section we shall review some of the string amplitudes derived from superstring perturbation theory and use those to obtain corrections to supergravity and super-Yang-Mills theory in the form of local effective interactions. Superstring perturbation theory uses a series expansion in powers of the string coupling
g_{s}
to evaluate quantum mechanical probability amplitudes, or simply amplitudes. The absolute value squared of an amplitude gives the quantum mechanical probability for a given process to occur. A process is specified by the data of the incoming and the outgoing string states, and the amplitude for the process is schematically given by the Feynman functional integral prescription of summing over all possible configurations of the string worldsheet given the initial and final state data.

The only topological information that remains, once the initial and final string data have been fixed by specifying the process, is the genus
h\geq 0
of the surface, namely the number of handles on the worldsheet. The string coupling
g_{s}
provides the weight given to each topology, so that the perturbative string amplitude
{\cal A}
for a given process is provided by the following topological expansion in powers of
g_{s}
,

\displaystyle{\cal A}=\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2h-2}{\cal A}^{(h)}+\hbox{ non-perturbative effects}

(12.1.1)

where
{\cal A}^{(h)}
is the genus
h
(or
h
-loop) contribution to the string amplitude
{\cal A}
. The expansion is schematically represented in Figure
11
. The prescription of the initial and final data for the incoming and/or outgoing string states will be discussed in subsection
12.4
.

g_{s}^{-2}
+\,\,g_{s}^{0}
+\,\,g_{s}^{2}
+\cdots

Figure 11
:
Schematic representation of the perturbative expansion in the string coupling
g_{s}
of the string amplitude
{\cal A}
for a process of four incoming and/or outgoing string states.

The construction of string amplitudes that we shall sketch below contains only bosonic states and the corresponding string theory is referred to as the closed bosonic string. To include fermions introduces many technical complications and has led to the elaboration of several possible different formulations, which are mostly beyond the scope of these lectures. Instead we shall focus on the bosonic string, and some mild extensions thereof, to illustrate the questions and results that relate to modular invariance of string perturbation theory in this section, to T-duality for toroidal compactifications in the next section
13
, and to S-duality in Type IIB superstring theory in section
14
.

12.2

String amplitudes as integrals over moduli space

Space-time is assumed to be a manifold
M
(or an orbifold) with a space-time metric
G
. Physically, the signature of
G
is Minkowskian. However, as is familiar from quantum field theory, it will often be convenient to construct string amplitudes in terms of a metric of Euclidean signature, so that
M
is a Riemannian manifold, and then analytically continue to Minkowski signature. String amplitudes are formulated in terms of two-dimensional orientable surfaces
\Sigma
and continuous maps
X:\Sigma\to M
. The Riemannian metric
G
on
M
induces a Riemannian metric
X^{*}(G)
on
\Sigma
under the pull-back
X^{*}
of the map
X
. Since
\Sigma
is orientable and carries a Riemannian metric it is automatically a Riemann surface.

The Polyakov formulation of string theory also invokes an intrinsic Riemannian metric
g
on
\Sigma
, which is independent of the induced metric
X^{*}(G)
. The map
X
and the intrinsic metric
g
on
\Sigma
are governed by a worldsheet action. String amplitudes are required to be independent of the coordinates used to parametrize
\Sigma
and
M
, which leads us to require invariance under the diffeomorphism groups
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm Diff}(M)
. A natural candidate satisfying these conditions is given by the following action,
22
22

22

Throughout,
\xi^{m}
with
m=1,2
are real local coordinates on
\Sigma
and
\partial_{m}=\partial/\partial\xi^{m}
are the derivatives with respect to
\xi^{m}
. The Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
takes the form
g=g_{mn}d\xi^{m}\otimes d\xi^{n}
while the volume form is given by
d\mu_{g}={1\over 2}\sqrt{{\rm det\,}g}\,\epsilon_{mn}d\xi^{m}\wedge d\xi^{n}
where
\epsilon_{mn}=-\epsilon_{nm}
specifies the orientation of
\Sigma
with
\epsilon_{12}=1
. Throughout, the Einstein summation convention on a pair of matching upper and lower indices will be implied, both for the worldsheet indices
m,n=1,2
as well as for the space-time indices
\mu,\nu=1,\cdots,\dim(M)
.

\displaystyle I_{G}[X,g]={1\over 4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,g^{mn}\partial_{m}X^{\mu}\partial_{n}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)

(12.2.1)

Feynman’s functional integral prescription for probability amplitudes gives the amplitudes as integrals over the spaces
{\rm Map}(\Sigma)
of maps
X
and
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)
of metrics
g
. The corresponding functional measures of integration
Dg
and
DX
may be constructed from suitable
L^{2}
norms on the tangent spaces to
{\rm Map}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)
. Denoting their tangent vectors by
\delta X
and
\delta g
respectively, the norms are dictated by
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm Diff}(M)
invariance,

\displaystyle\|\delta X\|^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,G_{\mu\nu}(x)\,\delta X^{\mu}\delta X^{\nu}\hskip 57.81621pt\|\delta g\|^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,g^{mn}\,g^{pq}\,\delta g_{mp}\,\delta g_{nq}

(12.2.2)

The partial integral over
{\rm Map}(\Sigma)
is given as follows,

\displaystyle e^{-W_{G}[g]}=\int_{{\rm Map}(\Sigma)}DX\,e^{-I_{G}[X,g]}

(12.2.3)

The two-dimensional quantum field theory defined by
I_{G}[X,g]
, for fixed metrics
g
and
G
, is referred to as a non-linear sigma model. The quantum field theory on
\Sigma
defined by the functional integral of
DX
depends on the space-time metric
G
, which is a function of
X
and therefore may depend on an infinite number of couplings. This may be seen explicitly by Taylor expanding
G
in powers of the field
X
in the neighborhood of a flat metric
G^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
,

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}(X)=G^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}+G^{(1)}_{\mu\nu;\rho}X^{\rho}+{1\over 2}G^{(2)}_{\mu\nu;\rho\sigma}X^{\rho}X^{\sigma}+{\cal O}(X^{3})

(12.2.4)

The sets of coefficients
G^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
,
G^{(1)}_{\mu\nu;\rho}
,
G^{(2)}_{\mu\nu;\rho\sigma},\cdots
may be viewed as independent coupling parameters of the quantum field theory corresponding to the non-linear sigma model. This theory is renormalizable in the sense of Friedan namely that, upon insisting on invariance under
{\rm Diff}(M)
, the number of counter-terms is finite at each order in
\alpha^{\prime}
.

The genus
h
contribution
{\cal A}^{(h)}
to the string amplitude for metric
G
at genus
h
is formally given by the functional integral over
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(h)}\sim\int_{{\rm Met}(\Sigma)}Dg\,e^{-W_{G}[g]}

(12.2.5)

where the symbol
\sim
indicates that this relation is formal. Taken literally, the integral over
g
would be divergent as the measure and the integrand are both invariant under
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
so that
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)
contains an infinite number of images of each metric
g
under
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
. Factoring out
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
amounts to a reduction of the integration to the quotient
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)/{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
of orbits, and may be carried out using the Faddeev-Popov gauge fixing procedure familiar from Yang-Mills gauge theory. The Faddeev-Popov determinant produced in the process may be represented by a functional integral over ghost fields
b
and
c
with action
I_{{\rm gh}}[b,c,g]
,

\displaystyle e^{-W_{{\rm gh}}[g]}=\int D(bc)e^{-I_{{\rm gh}}[b,c,g]}\hskip 57.81621ptI_{{\rm gh}}[b,c,g]={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\left(b\partial_{\bar{z}}c+{\rm c.c.}\right)

(12.2.6)

The resulting amplitude is then given by,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(h)}=\int_{{\rm Met}(\Sigma)/{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)}Dg\,e^{-W_{G}[g]-W_{{\rm gh}}[g]}

(12.2.7)

The reduction removes two of the three functional degrees of freedom of a two-dimensional metric, leaving a single real field. Such theories are referred to as non-critical strings.

Critical string theories are obtained by requiring that the combination of the measure
Dg
and the integrand are invariant also under Weyl transformations. The group of Weyl transformations
{\rm Weyl}(\Sigma)
leaves the metric
G
and the map
X
invariant, and multiplies the metric
g
by a positive scalar function,
g_{mn}(\xi)\to e^{2\sigma(\xi)}g_{mn}(\xi)
, where
\sigma:\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}
. The quotient of the space
{\rm Met}(\Sigma)
of metrics by the semi-direct product of
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm Weyl}(\Sigma)
may be identified with the moduli space
{\cal M}_{h}
of conformal structures on
\Sigma
, and is isomorphic to the space of complex structures on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
of genus
h
,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{h}={\rm Met}(\Sigma)/\big[{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)\ltimes{\rm Weyl}(\Sigma)\big]

(12.2.8)

Thus, our final expression for the genus
h
contribution
{\cal A}^{(h)}
to the amplitude for the critical string is given by a finite-dimensional integral over moduli space,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(h)}=\int_{{\cal M}_{h}}D\hat{g}\,e^{-W_{G}[g]-W_{{\rm gh}}[g]}

(12.2.9)

where
D\hat{g}
is the integration measure on moduli induced on the quotient.
The resulting amplitude
{\cal A}^{(h)}
is a function of the metric
G
on the space-time
M
.

12.3

Conformal invariance and decoupling negative norm states

The classical actions
I_{G}[X,g]
and
I_{{\rm gh}}[b,c,g]
are Weyl-invariant, but the measures
DX
,
Dg
, and
D(bc)
are not Weyl invariant, thereby producing Weyl anomalies. In particular, the functional
W_{G}[g]
, defined in (
12.2.1
), is not invariant under
{\rm Weyl}(\Sigma)
. For general metric
G
, the Weyl transformation law of
W_{G}
is complicated and not known explicitly. However, when the transformation law of
W_{G}
takes the following special form,

\displaystyle\delta W_{G}[g]={\mathfrak{c}\over 24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,R_{g}\,\delta\sigma

(12.3.1)

where
R_{g}
is the scalar curvature of the metric
g
and
\mathfrak{c}
is a constant, then
I_{G}
produces a conformal field theory with central charge
\mathfrak{c}
. For example, when
G
is the flat metric on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, then the action
I_{G}
is quadratic in
X
, and defines a conformal field theory with central charge
\mathfrak{c}=D
, and
I_{{\rm gh}}
is a conformal field theory with central charge
\mathfrak{c}=-26
. In the critical dimension
D=26
the combined field theory of
X,b,c
is therefore Weyl-invariant.

When the metric
G
is not flat, the Weyl transformation of
W_{G}
may be evaluated in an expansion in powers of
\alpha^{\prime}
, which is equivalent to an expansion in powers of the Riemann tensor of the metric
G
and its derivatives. To leading order in
\alpha^{\prime}
, one finds,

\displaystyle\delta W_{G}[g]={1\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,\left({D\over 6}R_{g}+{1\over 2}g^{mn}\partial_{m}\bar{X}^{\mu}\partial_{n}\bar{X}^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu}(\bar{X})\right)\delta\sigma+{\cal O}(\alpha^{\prime})

(12.3.2)

The first term is familiar from (
12.3.1
) for
\mathfrak{c}=D
. The second term involves the Ricci tensor
R
of the metric
G
and an arbitrary background field
\bar{X}
used to evaluate the quantum corrections in the background field method. The term in
R_{\mu\nu}
cancels when the metric
G
is Ricci flat. Thus we find the remarkable fact that the requirement of Weyl symmetry on the worldsheet metric
g
imposes Einstein’s equations on the space-time metric
G
.

Conformal symmetry plays a fundamental role in decoupling the negative norm and null states which arise in the Lorentz-covariant formulation of string theory in flat Minkowski space-time
M={\mathbb{R}}^{26}
. The maps
X:\Sigma\to M
satisfy the Laplace equation
\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}X^{\mu}=0
, so that the field
\partial_{z}X^{\mu}
is holomorphic, while the field
\partial_{\bar{z}}X^{\mu}
is anti-holomorphic. Both fields may be expanded in a Laurent series,

\displaystyle i\partial_{z}X^{\mu}=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}z^{-m-1}X^{\mu}_{m}\hskip 72.26999pt-i\partial_{\bar{z}}X^{\mu}=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\bar{z}^{-m-1}\tilde{X}^{\mu}_{m}

(12.3.3)

which upon canonical quantization satisfy,

\displaystyle(X^{\mu}_{m})^{\dagger}=X_{-m}^{\mu}

\displaystyle{}[X^{\mu}_{m},X^{\nu}_{n}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}

\displaystyle(\tilde{X}^{\mu}_{m})^{\dagger}=\tilde{X}_{-m}^{\mu}

\displaystyle{}[\tilde{X}^{\mu}_{m},\tilde{X}^{\nu}_{n}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}

(12.3.4)

while
X^{\mu}_{m}
and
\tilde{X}^{\nu}_{n}
commute with one another.
23
23

23

We note that the fields
\partial_{z}X^{\mu}
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}X^{\mu}
are treated as independent of one another, and not as complex conjugates of one another, a property that is inherited from the worldsheet with Minkowskian signature where the fields correspond to the data on the two light-cone directions.

The operators
X_{0}^{\mu}
and
\tilde{X}_{0}^{\mu}
are self-adjoint. They commute with one another and with all other modes and correspond to left and right moving momentum operators. (They will play a key role in toroidal compactification and will be discussed in greater detail in section
13
.) Thus the free fields
\partial_{z}X^{\mu}
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}X^{\mu}
decompose into
X_{0}^{\mu},\tilde{X}_{0}^{\mu}
in addition to an infinite number of decoupled harmonic oscillators
X_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{X}_{m}^{\mu}
for
m\not=0
, each of which satisfies a Heisenberg algebra.

The Fock space of the closed bosonic string in flat Minkowski space-time
{\mathbb{R}}^{26}
is constructed as follows. The operators
X_{0}^{\mu}
and
\tilde{X}_{0}^{\mu}
may be diagonalized simultaneously; their eigenvalues are real and are denoted
k^{\mu}
and
\tilde{k}^{\mu}
; their eigenvectors
|0;k\rangle
and
|0;\tilde{k}\rangle
are ground states of the string excitation spectrum of momenta
k^{\mu}
and
\tilde{k}^{\mu}
respectively. In flat Minkowski space-time, the left and right momenta must be equal
\tilde{k}^{\mu}=k^{\mu}
. (This condition will be relaxed for toroidal compactifications in order to accommodate winding modes, as will be discussed in section
13
.) The Fock space is the sum over string momenta
k\in{\mathbb{R}}^{26}
of the tensor product of left and right chiral Fock spaces,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{{\rm closed}}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{R}}^{26}}\mathfrak{F}_{k}\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}

(12.3.5)

The chiral Fock space
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
is the infinite tensor product of the Fock spaces of the harmonic oscillators
X_{n}^{\mu}
for
n\not=0
. Concretely, we define the ground state
|0;k\rangle
to satisfy,

\displaystyle X^{\mu}_{0}|0;k\rangle=k^{\mu}|0;k\rangle\hskip 72.26999ptX^{\mu}_{n}|0;k\rangle=0\hskip 21.68121ptn>0

(12.3.6)

The ground state is then a scalar under the Lorentz group and
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
is obtained as follows,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{k}~=\bigoplus_{{\ell\geq 0\atop n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{\ell}>0}}\Big\{x_{-n_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots x_{-n_{\ell}}^{\mu_{\ell}}|0;k\rangle\Big\}

(12.3.7)

with the analogous construction for
\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
. We define a norm on these spaces by normalizing the string ground state for given momentum by
\||0;k\rangle\|^{2}=1
. The lowest excited state in
\mathfrak{F}_{k}
with polarization vector
\varepsilon^{\mu}(k)
is given by
\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)X^{\mu}_{-1}|0;k\rangle
and has the following norm,

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)X^{\mu}_{-1}|0;k\rangle\|^{2}=\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)\varepsilon^{\mu}(k)

(12.3.8)

The norm is respectively positive, null, or negative when
\varepsilon^{\mu}(k)
is space-like, light-like, or time-like. Negative norm states are inconsistent with the principles of quantum mechanics, but they are unavoidable byproducts of the Lorentz-covariant quantization of the string, just as they are unavoidable in the covariant quantization of gauge theories.

In flat 26-dimensional space-time negative norm states and null states may be decoupled with the help of conformal symmetry, whose Virasoro generators take the form,

\displaystyle L_{m}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{1\over 2}X_{m-n}\cdot X_{n}\hskip 72.26999ptL_{0}={1\over 2}X_{0}^{2}+\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{-n}\cdot X_{n}

(12.3.9)

The conformal transformations by the Virasoro algebras act as follows on the oscillators,

\displaystyle{}[L_{m},X_{n}^{\mu}]=-nX^{\mu}_{m+n}

(12.3.10)

and similarly for the tilde operators. One defines the subspace of physical states
\mathfrak{F}^{{\rm phys}}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{k}
by imposing the following conditions on the states
|\psi\rangle\in\mathfrak{F}_{k}
,

\displaystyle(L_{0}-1)|\psi\rangle=L_{m}|\psi\rangle=0\hskip 72.26999ptm>0

(12.3.11)

All negative norm states are eliminated by these physical state conditions, and all null states decouple, just as is the case when imposing Gauss’s law in gauge theory. The ground state is physical provided
k^{2}=2
which makes it a tachyon. The tachyon renders the bosonic string vacuum unstable but it is eliminated in the superstring, and we shall omit its discussion in the sequel. Returning to the example of the first excited state,
|\psi\rangle=\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)X^{\mu}_{-1}|0;k\rangle
, we see that the physical state conditions
L_{m}|\psi\rangle=0
for
m\geq 2
are automatically satisfied, while those for
m=1,0
impose the constraints
k\cdot\varepsilon(k)=0
and the massless condition
k^{2}=0
on the momenta. The resulting physical states constitute the spectrum of the open bosonic string and include a massless vector particle, such as the photon.

The above construction of the Fock space also shows that the closed bosonic string spectrum contains the massless states
\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(k)X_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{X}_{-1}^{\nu}|0;k\rangle
where
k^{2}=0
and
k\cdot\varepsilon=k\cdot\tilde{\varepsilon}=0
. The traceless symmetric part of
\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(k)
corresponds to the graviton state as expected from the presence of the metric
G
in the worldsheet action. However, there is also a trace-part which corresponds to the scalar dilaton state, and an anti-symmetric part which corresponds to a rank-two anti-symmetric state. These additional states and particles are not required by general relativity, but their presence in the spectrum will, in fact, be mandated by supergravity, as we shall see in section
14
.

In view of the presence of the extra states of the dilaton and rank two anti-symmetric tensor field, we may generalize the non-linear sigma model action (
12.2.1
) to include a field
\Phi
for the dilaton as well as a rank two anti-symmetric field
B_{\mu\nu}
,
24
24

24

The factor of
i
multiplying the
B_{\mu\nu}
term is present for worldsheet metric
\mathfrak{g}
of Euclidean signature, but is absent when Minkowski signature is used.

\displaystyle I_{G,\Phi,B}[X,g]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\bigg\{\Big(g^{mn}G_{\mu\nu}(X)\!-i\varepsilon^{mn}B_{\mu\nu}(X)\Big)\partial_{m}X^{\mu}\partial_{n}X^{\nu}

(12.3.12)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2\alpha^{\prime}\,R_{g}\,\Phi(X)\bigg\}\qquad

where
R_{g}
is the scalar curvature of the metric
g
, already encountered in (
12.3.1
). In the presence of the fields
G,\Phi,B
conformal invariance requires, up to lowest non-trivial order in the
\alpha^{\prime}
-expansion, a generalization of Einstein’s equations that includes the dynamics of the fields
\Phi
and
B_{\mu\nu}
. Those field equations may be derived from the following action,

\displaystyle S[G,\Phi,B]={1\over 2\kappa^{2}}\int d\mu_{G}\,e^{-2\Phi}\left(R_{G}+4D_{\mu}\Phi D^{\mu}\Phi-{1\over 12}H_{\mu\nu\rho}H^{\mu\nu\rho}\right)

(12.3.13)

where
\kappa
is a constant,
D_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative, and
H_{\mu\nu\rho}=D_{\mu}B_{\nu\rho}+D_{\nu}B_{\rho\mu}+D_{\rho}B_{\mu\nu}
.

In summary, conformal symmetry is the local gauge symmetry that eliminates negative norm states and decouples null states. In this section we have illustrated this mechanism when space-time is flat, but the result is expected to hold in space-times which are asymptotically flat or anti-de Sitter.

12.4

String amplitudes in terms of vertex operators

Conformal symmetry also allows us to simplify the formulation of string scattering processes. Instead of representing each incoming and outgoing string in a scattering process by a boundary of the string worldsheet, as was presented in Figure
11
, we can encode all the physical information of the incoming or outgoing strings in terms of vertex operators. The data for these vertex operators is specified at vertex insertion points on the worldsheet, as illustrated in Figure
12
. One may view these insertion points as the limit in which the radii of the boundary discs of Figure
11
are shrunk to zero by a conformal transformation.

g_{s}^{-2}
+\,\,g_{s}^{0}
+\,\,g_{s}^{2}
+\cdots

Figure 12
:
Schematic representation of the perturbative expansion in the string coupling
g_{s}
formulated in terms of vertex operator insertions on the worldsheet.

Consider the scattering of
N
on-shell gravitons in flat space-time
{\mathbb{R}}^{26}
, with momenta
k_{i}
and polarization tensors
\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}(k)
for
i=1,\cdots,N
. A graviton of momentum
k_{i}
introduce a small ripple on the metric of space-time, given in terms of a plane monochromatic wave. Adding up the contributions of the
N
gravitons the space-time metric is perturbed as follows to lowest order in the amplitude of the wave given by the polarizations tensors
\varepsilon_{i}
,

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}(X)=\eta_{\mu\nu}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}(k)\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot X}+{\cal O}(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})

(12.4.1)

Conformal invariance requires
G
to satisfy Einstein’s equations, as was discussed in the second paragraph of subsection
12.3
. Einstein’s equations apply in linearized form, because we consider the metric only to first order in each
\varepsilon_{i}
, and impose the conditions
k_{i}^{2}=0
and
k_{i}^{\mu}\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}(k)=0
. Substituting the expression (
12.4.1
) for the metric fluctuations into the action
I_{G}[X,g]
of (
12.2.1
) and the functional integral of (
12.2.9
), and retaining the contribution which is linear in each
\varepsilon_{i}
gives the genus
h
contribution to the amplitude for the scattering of
N
gravitons in terms of vertex operators,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(h)}=\int_{{\cal M}_{h}}D\hat{g}\int_{{\rm Map}(\Sigma)}DX\,{\cal V}_{1}[X,g]\cdots{\cal V}_{N}[X,g]\,e^{-I_{G}[X,g]-W_{{\rm gh}}[g]}

(12.4.2)

The vertex operators are read off the expansion in
\varepsilon_{i}
to linear order and are given by,

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{i}[X,g]=\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}(k)\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,g^{mn}\partial_{m}X^{\mu}\partial_{n}X^{\nu}\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot X}

(12.4.3)

The physical state conditions for the graviton, namely its massless condition
k_{i}^{2}=0
, and the transversality of its polarization tensors
k^{\mu}_{i}\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}(k)=0
, and
\varepsilon_{i;\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu\nu}=0
, guarantee that the unintegrated vertex operator
g^{mn}\partial_{m}X^{\mu}\partial_{n}X^{\nu}\,e^{ik_{i}\cdot X}
has conformal dimension
(1,1)
and may be integrated over
\Sigma
consistently with conformal symmetry. One may proceed analogously for the fields
\Phi
and
B_{\mu\nu}
.

12.5

Superstring amplitudes

Scattering amplitudes for the superstrings may similarly be formulated in terms of functional integrals over the string degrees of freedom, such as
X^{\mu},b,c
, involving vertex operators. Unlike for the case of the bosonic string, several different formulations have been developed over time for the case of the superstring, each with certain advantages and disadvantages. Of central concern to us here is not so much how the amplitudes have been obtained but rather what their structure is. Thus, we shall proceed below by simply quoting the final expressions for the amplitudes we need, and refer to the literature for their detailed derivations, which are often quite involved. We shall concentrate on Type II theories here, but a similar analysis may be carried out for Type I and Heterotic superstrings.

For definiteness, we shall consider the scattering process that involves four gravitons, whose momenta we denote by
k_{i}
and whose polarization tensors are chosen in factorized form
\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu}(k)\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\nu}(k)
with
k_{i}^{2}=k_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{i}=k_{i}\cdot\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}=0
and
i=1,2,3,4
. It will be useful to introduce the dimensionless kinematic Lorentz-invariants defined by,

\displaystyle s_{ij}=-{\alpha^{\prime}\over 4}(k_{i}+k_{j})^{2}

(12.5.1)

These variables satisfy a number of kinematic relations as a result of momentum conservation and the massless conditions
k_{i}^{2}=0
, which may be solved as follows,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=s_{12}=s_{34}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=s_{14}=s_{23}

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=s_{13}=s_{24}

\displaystyle s+t+u

\displaystyle=0

(12.5.2)

Closed superstring perturbation theory produces the following on-shell four-graviton amplitude in Type II superstring theory,

\displaystyle{\cal A}(\varepsilon_{i};k_{i})=\kappa_{10}^{2}\,{\cal R}^{4}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2h-2}{\cal A}^{(h)}(s_{ij})

(12.5.3)

where
{\cal R}
stands for the on-shell linearized Riemann tensor,
{\cal R}^{4}
for a particular scalar invariant constructed out of
{\cal R}
, and
{\cal A}^{(h)}
is a Lorentz scalar function that depends only on
s,t,u
. The fact that a single kinematic combination
{\cal R}^{4}
is shared by the amplitudes at all loop order is a consequence of the space-time supersymmetry of the Type II superstring, and does not hold for other superstring theories such as Type I or Heterotic strings. The particular structure of the Lorentz contractions needed to form the scalar
{\cal R}^{4}
is also a direct consequence of supersymmetry. In the factorized basis for the polarization tensors for the gravitons, the combination
{\cal R}^{4}
is itself factorized,

\displaystyle 2^{6}\,{\cal R}^{4}={\cal K}\,\tilde{\cal K}

(12.5.4)

where
{\cal K}
depends only on the polarization vectors
\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu}
and
\tilde{\cal K}
depends only on the polarization vectors
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\mu}
. In terms of the linearized field strengths,

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{i}^{\mu}\varepsilon_{i}^{\nu}-k_{i}^{\nu}\varepsilon_{i}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{i}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{i}^{\mu}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\nu}-k_{i}^{\nu}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{\mu}

(12.5.5)

the factor
{\cal K}
is given as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f_{1}f_{2})(f_{3}f_{4})+(f_{1}f_{4})(f_{2}f_{3})+(f_{1}f_{3})(f_{2}f_{4})

(12.5.6)

\displaystyle-4(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}f_{4})-4(f_{1}f_{2}f_{4}f_{3})-4(f_{1}f_{3}f_{2}f_{4})

where the parentheses are defined by
(f_{i}f_{j})=f_{i}^{\mu\nu}f_{j}^{\nu\mu}
and
(f_{i}f_{j}f_{k}f_{\ell})=f_{i}^{\mu\nu}f_{j}^{\nu\rho}f_{k}^{\rho\sigma}f_{\ell}^{\sigma\mu}
. The expression for
\tilde{\cal K}
is given by the above expression with
f\to\tilde{f}
.

Explicit formulas for
{\cal A}^{(h)}
have been established from first principles for tree-level
h=0
, one-loop
h=1
, and two loops
h=2
, and are given as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(0)}(s_{ij})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over stu}{\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(1-t)\Gamma(1-u)\over\Gamma(1+s)\Gamma(1+t)\Gamma(1+u)}

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\pi\over 16}\int_{{\cal M}_{1}}{|d\tau|^{2}\over({\rm Im\,}\tau)^{2}}\,{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(2)}(s_{ij})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\pi\over 64}\int_{{\cal M}_{2}}{|d\Omega|^{2}\over({\rm det\,}{\rm Im\,}\Omega)^{3}}\,{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)

(12.5.7)

The integrands
{\cal B}^{(1)}
and
{\cal B}^{(2)}
are dimensionless Lorentz scalar functions of
s,t,u
given by,

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma^{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}{d^{2}z_{i}\over{\rm Im\,}\tau}\,\exp\left\{\sum_{i<j}s_{ij}G(z_{i},z_{j}|\tau)\right\}

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma^{4}}{|{\cal Y}|^{2}\over({\rm det\,}{\rm Im\,}\Omega)^{2}}\,\exp\left\{\sum_{i<j}s_{ij}{\cal G}(z_{i},z_{j}|\Omega)\right\}

(12.5.8)

Here,
z_{i}
are the vertex insertion points on the surface
\Sigma
,
\tau
is the modulus used here as a local complex coordinate for the genus-one moduli space
{\cal M}_{1}
, and
\Omega
is the period matrix used here as a set of local coordinates for genus-two moduli space
{\cal M}_{2}
, both of which are defined in appendix
B
. Furthermore, in the genus-two amplitude,
{\cal Y}
is a holomorphic
(1,0)
form in each one of the vertex points
z_{i}
given by,

\displaystyle{\cal Y}=(t-u)\Delta(1,2)\Delta(3,4)+(s-t)\Delta(1,3)\Delta(4,2)+(u-s)\Delta(1,4)\Delta(2,3)

(12.5.9)

where
\Delta
is a holomorphic
(1,0)
form in its entries defined by,

\displaystyle\Delta(i,j)=\omega_{1}(z_{i})\omega_{2}(z_{j})-\omega_{2}(z_{i})\omega_{1}(z_{j})

(12.5.10)

and
\omega_{I}(z)
are the canonically normalized holomorphic Abelian differentials on a Riemann surface of genus 2 (see appendix
B
). In
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
, the Green function
G(x_{i},z_{j}|\tau)
is the scalar Green function on the torus of modulus
\tau
defined in (
5.5.9
) and given explicitly either in terms of Kronecker-Eisenstein series in (
5.5.8
) or Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions in (
5.5.14
). In
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
, the Green function
{\cal G}(z_{i},z_{j}|\Omega)
is the Arakelov Green function on the genus-two Riemann surface
\Sigma
with period matrix
\Omega
, which we shall now construct.

Analogous formulas hold for amplitudes with more than four external states, as well as for Type I and Heterotic strings.

12.5.1

The Arakelov Green function for genus 2 Riemann surfaces

To construct the Arakelov Green function on a genus-two Riemann surface
\Sigma
, we begin by defining the canonical Kähler form
\kappa
on
\Sigma
which is given by the pull-back under the Abel map of the canonical Kähler form on the Jacobian
J(\Sigma)
(see appendix
D
),

\displaystyle\kappa={i\over 4}\sum_{I,J=1}^{2}\big(Y^{-1})^{IJ}\omega_{I}\wedge\bar{\omega}_{J}\hskip 72.26999pt\int_{\Sigma}\kappa=1

(12.5.11)

where
Y={\rm Im\,}\Omega
. In terms of local complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
on
\Sigma
, we have
\kappa={i\over 2}\kappa_{z\bar{z}}dz\wedge d\bar{z}
, and the Arakelov Green function is defined by,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}\partial_{z}{\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)=-\pi\delta(z,w)+\pi\kappa_{z\bar{z}}(z)\hskip 36.135pt\int_{\Sigma}\kappa(z){\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)=0

(12.5.12)

It may be constructed explicitly in terms of the prime form and Abelian integrals,

\displaystyle{\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(z,w|\Omega)-\gamma(z|\Omega)-\gamma(w|\Omega)+\gamma^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle G(z,w|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\ln|E(z,w|\Omega)|^{2}+2\pi{\rm Im\,}\left(\int_{w}^{z}\omega_{I}\right)(Y^{-1})^{IJ}{\rm Im\,}\left(\int_{w}^{z}\omega_{J}\right)

(12.5.13)

where,

\displaystyle\gamma(z|\Omega)=\int_{\Sigma}\kappa(w)G(z,w|\Omega)\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma^{1}(\Omega)=\int_{\Sigma}\kappa(z)\gamma(z|\Omega)

(12.5.14)

12.5.2

Physical singularity structure of the four-graviton amplitudes

The singularity structure of the genus-one and genus-two amplitudes is as follows.
For fixed moduli, the singularities of
{\cal B}^{(h)}
as a function of
s,t,u
are governed by the operator product expansion of the graviton vertex operators, namely the conformal field theory of free scalar fields on
\Sigma
. The Green functions
G(z,w|\tau)
and
{\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)
are smooth throughout
\Sigma
, except for a logarithmic singularity at
z=w
where they both behave as
-\ln|z-w|^{2}
plus regular terms. As a result, the integrations which define
{\cal B}^{(h)}
are absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s_{ij})<1
. The analytic continuation of
{\cal B}^{(h)}
may be carried out to the complex plane in each variable and produces simple poles at positive integers
s,t,u\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

The further integration over moduli, required to obtain the physical amplitude
{\cal A}^{(h)}
for
h=1,2
, is absolutely convergent only when
{\rm Re\,}(s_{ij})=0
. Analytic continuation in
s_{ij}
to the complex plane may be carried out using a decomposition of moduli space into subregions in each of which the domain of convergence is enlarged. This procedure has been carried out explicitly only for genus one. The resulting analytic continuation has branch cuts in
s_{ij}
starting at any non-negative integer, which signals two-particle intermediate states. The analytic continuation process will be illustrated in the low energy expansion, near the massless branch cut starting at
s_{ij}=0
below.

12.6

Ultraviolet finiteness from modular invariance

An alternative presentation of the genus-one and genus-two amplitudes may be given by introducing internal loop momenta. We shall illustrate this here for the case of genus one. One begins by choosing a canonical homology basis of cycles
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
on the torus
\Sigma
, and chooses the loop momentum
p
to flow through the cycle
\mathfrak{A}
. The amplitude is then given in terms of a Hermitian pairing between left and right chiral amplitudes,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{10}}dp\int_{{\cal M}_{1}}\int_{\Sigma^{4}}{\cal F}(z_{i},k_{i},p|\tau)\,\overline{{\cal F}(z_{i},-k_{i},p|\tau)}

(12.6.1)

where we have suppressed the dependence on the polarization tensors.
The chiral amplitude
{\cal F}(z_{i},k_{i},p|\tau)
is locally holomorphic in
\tau
and in
z_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,4
. Its explicit form is given by the following top holomorphic differential form on
{\cal M}_{1}\times\Sigma^{4}
,

\displaystyle{\cal F}(z_{i},k_{i},p|\tau)=e^{i\pi\tau p^{2}+2\pi i\sum_{i}k_{i}z_{i}}\prod_{i<j}\vartheta_{1}(z_{i}-z_{j}|\tau)^{-s_{ij}}d\tau\prod_{i=1}^{4}dz_{i}

(12.6.2)

The price to pay for the local holomorphicity is that
{\cal F}
has non-trivial monodromy when a point
z_{\ell}
is taken around one of the homology cycles of the surface, and we have,

\displaystyle{\cal F}(z_{i}+\delta_{i,\ell}\,\mathfrak{A},k_{i},p|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi ik_{\ell}\cdot p}\,{\cal F}(z_{i},k_{i},p|\tau)

\displaystyle{\cal F}(z_{i}+\delta_{i,\ell}\,\mathfrak{B},k_{i},p|\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal F}(z_{i},k_{i},p+k_{\ell}|\tau)

(12.6.3)

When the point
z_{\ell}
is taken around the point
z_{i}
with
\ell\not=i
, the chiral amplitude
{\cal F}
is multiplied by a phase factor
e^{-2\pi is_{ij}}
. Modular transformations of
{\cal F}
involve a change of homology basis
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
and thus a change of momentum routing through the torus. The Hermitian pairing of
{\cal F}
and
\bar{\cal F}
is familiar from two-dimensional conformal field theory where the loop momentum
p
labels the conformal blocks of ten copies of a
c=1
theory.

Thanks to modular invariance, all string amplitudes are ultraviolet (UV) finite. The result was obtained by Shapiro for the bosonic string at genus one, but holds for all modular invariant superstrings to all genera.

We can illustrate the mechanism by which the genus-one string amplitude is UV finite by inspection of the chiral amplitude derived in the previous paragraph. For any string theory, the chiral amplitude, with loop momentum
p
chosen to flow through the cycle
\mathfrak{A}
, has the following universal pre-factor which is Gaussian in the loop momentum,

\displaystyle{\cal F}(z_{i},\varepsilon_{i},k_{i},p|\tau)=e^{i\pi\tau p^{2}}\times\hbox{ exponentials and powers in }p

(12.6.4)

The Gaussian factor is universal in the sense that it is independent of the momenta
k_{i}
and polarization tensors
\varepsilon_{i}
of the external states and actually independent of the specific string theory under consideration. Modular invariance allows one to choose the fundamental domain
{\cal M}_{1}
for
{\cal H}/SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
that is adapted to the particular choice for the flow of loop momentum. For our choice of loop momentum traversing the
\mathfrak{A}
-cycle, the choice of fundamental domain is the standard one,
{\cal M}_{1}=\{\tau\in{\cal H},~|\tau|\geq 1,~|{\rm Re\,}(\tau)|\leq{1\over 2}\}
. The high energy behavior of the loop momentum is governed by the magnitude of
{\rm Im\,}(\tau)
, which is uniformly bounded away from zero by our choice of fundamental domain. The uniform Gaussian suppression at large loop momenta implies UV finiteness as all other factors are either only exponential or polynomial in
p
.
Higher genus amplitudes have a generalization of the above factor which involves the period matrix of the corresponding higher genus surface, but the structure is otherwise analogous to the one-loop case.

12.7

Effective interactions from the four-graviton amplitude

The tree-level, one-loop, and two-loop string amplitudes listed in (
12.5
) for four gravitons have important applications to the evaluation of string theory corrections to supergravity in the low energy limit. Type IIB supergravity and its relation to Type IIB superstring theory will be reviewed in section
14
. In preparation for those discussions and the comparison of perturbative results with the implications from supersymmetry and
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-duality of Type IIB string theory, we shall here evaluate the behavior of the partial amplitudes in (
12.5
) and the full amplitudes in (
12.5
) in the low energy limit.

To illustrate the emergence of low energy effective interactions from superstring amplitudes, we recall the expansion in powers of the string coupling
g_{s}
of the four-graviton amplitude, given in (
12.5.3
) and repeated here for convenience,

\displaystyle{\cal A}(\varepsilon_{i};k_{i})=\kappa_{10}^{2}{\cal R}^{4}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2h-2}{\cal A}^{(h)}(s,t,u)

(12.7.1)

where
{\cal R}
stands for the on-shell linearized Weyl tensor, whose expression was given in (
12.5.4
) and (
12.5.6
), while the expressions for
{\cal A}^{(0)},{\cal A}^{(1)},{\cal A}^{(2)}
were given in (
12.5
).

12.7.1

Low energy expansion at tree-level

We concentrate first on the tree-level contribution
{\cal A}^{(0)}
and use the following series expansion for the ratio of
\Gamma
-functions,

\displaystyle{\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(1-t)\Gamma(1-u)\over\Gamma(1+s)\Gamma(1+t)\Gamma(1+u)}=\exp\left\{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{2\zeta(2n+1)\over 2n+1}(s^{2n+1}+t^{2n+1}+u^{2n+1})\right\}

(12.7.2)

The argument of the exponential must clearly be odd in
s,t,u
, since the left side is mapped to its inverse under simultaneous sign reversal of
s,t,u
. The linear term cancels by
s+t+u=0
. The amplitude
{\cal A}^{(0)}
is a symmetric function of
s,t,u
and may therefore be expanded in powers of the two remaining symmetric polynomials in
s,t,u
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}=s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2}\hskip 72.26999pt\sigma_{3}=s^{3}+t^{3}+u^{3}=3stu

(12.7.3)

The first few terms in the expansion in powers of
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
are given by,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(0)}={1\over stu}+2\zeta(3)+\sigma_{2}\,\zeta(5)+{2\over 3}\sigma_{3}\,\zeta(3)^{2}+{1\over 2}\sigma_{2}^{2}\,\zeta(7)+{\cal O}(s_{ij})^{5}

(12.7.4)

The first term on the right side in the expansion is non-analytic at low energy and corresponds to the exchange of massless gravitons and other massless bosons in Type IIB supergravity. The remaining terms on the right side are all local and successively correspond to effective interactions which are schematically of the form
D^{2k}{\cal R}^{4}
for
k=0,2,3
and
4
. The effective interaction
D^{2}{\cal R}^{4}
is missing in this list because of the relation
s+t+u=0
.

12.7.2

Transcendental weight

An important notion that has arisen over the past few years, starting in the study of quantum field theory amplitudes, is that of transcendental weight. A natural starting point for its definition is with zeta-values and multiple zeta-values,

\displaystyle\zeta(k)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n^{k}}\hskip 72.26999pt\zeta(k_{1},\cdots,k_{p})=\sum_{n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\cdots\geq n_{p}\geq 1}{1\over n_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots n_{p}^{k_{p}}}

(12.7.5)

for integer
k,k_{1}\geq 2
and
k_{2},\cdots,k_{p}\geq 1
. When
k
is even,
\zeta(k)\in\pi^{k}{\mathbb{Q}}
. Transcendental weight provides a grading upon multiplication and one assigns weight 0 to any algebraic number, weight 1 to
\pi
, and thus weight
k
to
\zeta(k)
for
k
even. By extension, one assigns weight
k
to
\zeta(k)
for all
k
and weight
k=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{p}
to the multiple zeta value
\zeta(k_{1},\cdots,k_{p})
.

With these assignments of transcendental weight, we observe that the argument of the exponential on the right of (
12.7.2
) has transcendental weight 0 provided we assign weight
-1
to
s,t,
and
u
. Each term in the expansion of the tree-level amplitude in (
12.7.4
) thus has weight 3. The amplitude is said to exhibit uniform transcendentality.

Remarkably, string theory has non-trivial transcendentality properties already at tree-level. This is in contrast with the case of quantum field theory where tree-level amplitudes have trivial transcendentality and it is only through the expansion of the
\Gamma
-functions that arise in dimensional regularization at loop level that non-trivial transcendentality arises. Uniform transcendentality, observed here for tree-level Type II string amplitudes, is shared in quantum field theory by the maximally supersymmetric
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theory in four dimensions. A thorough understanding of how this property arises, and for precisely which correlators it holds, remains an open problem.

12.8

Genus one in terms of modular graph functions

The genus-one amplitude
{\cal A}^{(1)}
is obtained in (
12.5
) by integrating the partial amplitude
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
, which is a modular function in
\tau
, over the genus-one moduli space
{\cal M}_{1}
. The partial amplitude in turn is given by the integration over four copies of the torus
\Sigma
with modulus
\tau
in (
12.5
).
For fixed
\tau\in{\cal H}
, the scalar Green function
G(z|\tau)
on the torus is smooth throughout the torus except for the logarithmic singularity at
z=0
given by
G(z|\tau)\approx-\ln|z|^{2}
. As a result, for fixed
\tau
, the integrals which define
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
as a function of
s,t,u
are absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s),{\rm Re\,}(t),{\rm Re\,}(u)<1
, and may be expanded in a Taylor series in powers of
s,t,u
with radius of convergence
|s|,|t|,|u|<1
. Since the function
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
is symmetric in
s,t,u
, we may organize this expansion in powers of the symmetric polynomials
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
in
s,t,u
as we did for the tree-level amplitude,

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(1)}(\tau)\,{\sigma_{2}^{p}\,\sigma_{3}^{q}\over p!\,q!}

(12.8.1)

Since
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
is a modular function in
\tau
each coefficient
{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(1)}(\tau)
is a modular function of
\tau
. The coefficients may be computed by expanding the exponential in the integrand of (
12.5
) in powers of its argument to a given order
w
, which is the overall degree in the variables
s,t,u
, given by
w=2p+3q
, so that we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{{p,q\geq 0\atop 2p+3q=w}}{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(1)}(\tau)\,{\sigma_{2}^{p}\,\sigma_{3}^{q}\over p!\,q!}={1\over w!}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\int_{\Sigma}{d^{2}z_{i}\over{\rm Im\,}\tau}\,\left(\sum_{i<j}s_{ij}G(z_{i}-z_{j}|\tau)\right)^{w}

(12.8.2)

The coefficients
{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(1)}(\tau)
for different values of
p,q
satisfying
w=2p+3q
may then be sorted out by further expanding the
w
-th power on the right side. The expansion may be presented graphically, and the modular function corresponding to a given graph is precisely one of the modular graph functions discussed in section
9
. Each graph has four vertices, namely the points
z_{i}
for
i=1,2,3,4
, and
w
edges. An edge may connect any pair of distinct vertices, but is not allowed to begin and end on the same vertex.

The expansion is simplified by the following observations. Any modular graph function corresponding to

•

a graph containing a vertex on which only a single edge begins or ends vanishes;

•

a graph which becomes disconnected upon removing one edge vanishes;

•

a graph which becomes disconnected upon removing a single vertex factorizes into the modular graph functions of the resulting connected components.

The graphs corresponding to the modular graph functions which contribute to the modular functions
{\cal B}_{(p,q)}(\tau)
up to weight
w=5
are presented in Figure
13
. Instead of organizing the graphs by weight
w
, they may alternatively be organized by loop order, which is indicated in Figure
13
by the colored rectangular boxes.

The weight
w
of the graphs contributing to
{\cal B}_{(p,q)}(\tau)
for
w=2p+3q
may now be seen to coincide with the definition of transcendental weight given in subsection
12.7.2
, provided we continue to assign weight
-1
to
s,t,u
and weight 0 to
\tau
.

By expanding (
12.8.2
) and using identities such as the one in (
9.2
), one finds,

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(0,0)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(1,0)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(0,1)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{5}{3}E_{3}+\tfrac{1}{3}\zeta(3)

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(2,0)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2C_{2,1,1}+E_{2}^{2}-E_{4}

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(1,1)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{7}{3}C_{3,1,1}+\tfrac{5}{3}E_{2}E_{3}+\tfrac{1}{3}\zeta(3)E_{3}-\tfrac{34}{15}E_{5}+\tfrac{1}{5}\zeta(5)

(12.8.3)

up to weight
w=5
included. The graphs that contribute are shown in Figure

.

12.9

Genus two in terms of modular graph functions

The genus-two amplitude
{\cal A}^{(2)}
is obtained in (
12.5
) by integrating
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
over the genus-two moduli space
{\cal M}_{2}
. The partial amplitude
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
in turn is given by the integration over four copies on the genus-two surface
\Sigma
with moduli
\Omega
in (
12.5
). For fixed
\Omega\in{\cal H}_{2}
, the scalar Arakelov Green function
{\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)
is smooth throughout
\Sigma
except for the logarithmic singularity at
z=w
given by
{\cal G}(z,w|\Omega)\approx-\ln|z-w|^{2}
. As a result, for fixed
\Omega
, the integrals that define
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
as a function of
s,t,u
are absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s),{\rm Re\,}(t),{\rm Re\,}(u)<1
and may be expanded in a Taylor series in powers of
s,t,u
with radius of convergence
|s|,|t|,|u|<1
. This situation is completely analogous to the genus-one case. The presence of the additional measure factor
|{\cal Y}|^{2}
is inconsequential for these results to hold as
{\cal Y}
is holomorphic on
\Sigma
.

Since the function
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
is symmetric in
s,t,u
, we may organize its Taylor expansion in powers of
s,t,u
via the symmetric polynomials
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
as we did for the tree-level amplitude and the genus-one partial amplitude,

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(2)}(\Omega)\,{\sigma_{2}^{p}\,\sigma_{3}^{q}\over p!\,q!}

(12.9.1)

Since
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
is a modular function of
\Omega
, namely it is invariant under the genus-two modular group
Sp(4,{\mathbb{Z}})
, each coefficient
{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(2)}(\Omega)
is itself a modular function of
\Omega
. The coefficients may be computed by expanding the exponential in the integrand of (
12.5
) in powers of its argument to a given order
w
, which is related to the overall degree in the variables
s,t,u
, given by
w+2=2p+3q
, so that we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{{p,q\geq 0\atop 2p+3q=w+2}}{\cal B}_{(p,q)}^{(2)}(\Omega)\,{\sigma_{2}^{p}\,\sigma_{3}^{q}\over p!\,q!}={1\over w!}\int_{\Sigma^{4}}{|{\cal Y}|^{2}\over({\rm det\,}{\rm Im\,}\Omega)^{2}}\left(\sum_{i<j}s_{ij}{\cal G}(z_{i},z_{j}|\Omega)\right)^{w}

(12.9.2)

Note that the shift to
w+2
included in the range of summation on the left side is due to the fact that
{\cal Y}
is linear in the variables
s,t,u
.

12.9.1

Contributions to low weight

Linearity of
{\cal Y}
in
s,t,u
immediately implies that the genus-two contribution to the
{\cal R}^{4}
interaction without derivatives must vanish
{\cal B}_{(0,0)}^{(2)}=0
. As was already mentioned earlier, the
D^{2}{\cal R}^{4}
contribution vanishes in view of the momentum conservation identity
s+t+u=0
.

The leading low energy contribution is the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
interaction obtained from the zero-th order expansion term of the exponential in (
12.8.2
). The remaining integrations required to evaluate
{\cal B}^{(2)}_{(1,0)}
may be carried out using the Riemann bilinear relations, reviewed in appendix
B
, and yield an
\Omega
-independent contribution.

The coefficient
{\cal B}^{(2)}_{(0,1)}
has non-trivial dependence on
\Omega
, denoted by
64\,\varphi(\Omega)
. Assembling these results to order
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
we have,

\displaystyle{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)=32(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})+192\,stu\,\varphi(\Omega)+{\cal O}(s_{ij}^{4})

(12.9.3)

The genus-two modular function
\varphi(\Omega)
is given in terms of the Arakelov Green function by,

\displaystyle\varphi(\Omega)={1\over 8}\int_{\Sigma^{2}}\omega_{I}(x)\omega_{J}(y)\Big(\bar{\omega}^{I}(x)\bar{\omega}^{J}(y)-2\bar{\omega}^{J}(x)\bar{\omega}^{I}(y)\Big){\cal G}(x,y|\Omega)

(12.9.4)

where
\bar{\omega}^{I}=(Y^{-1})^{IJ}\,\overline{\omega_{J}}
. This object was identified with the spectral invariant introduced by Kawazumi and Zhang. Higher order contributions to
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
may similarly be expressed in terms of integrals of products of Arakelov Green functions, and may be viewed as higher genus generalizations of the genus-one modular graph functions investigated in section
9
. Their general structure has, however, been less well-studied and is much less well-understood than that of their genus-one counterparts.

12.10

Integration over the genus-one moduli space

When considering one-loop or two-loop amplitudes we have, so far, focused attention on the structure of the partial
amplitudes
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
and
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
, for which we have obtained a systematic low energy expansion in terms of modular graph functions, both at genus one and genus two. For fixed
\tau
and
\Omega
, this expansion is absolutely convergent for
{\rm Re\,}(s),{\rm Re\,}(t),{\rm Re\,}(u)<1
, and admits an analytic continuation in
s,t,u
whose sole singularities are simple poles at positive integers in
s,t,u
, corresponding to the exchanges of massive string states, as may be seen from the operator product expansion.

Obtaining the corresponding physical string amplitudes
{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})
and
{\cal A}^{(2)}(s_{ij})
further requires the integrations over the moduli spaces of genus-one and genus-two Riemann surfaces shown in (
12.5
). These integrations are absolutely convergent only when
s,t,u
are purely imaginary, in which case
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
and
{\cal B}^{(2)}(s,t,u|\Omega)
may be uniformly bounded on their respective moduli spaces. For the case of genus one, the lack of convergence originates from the integration in the region near the cusp. This may be seen by expressing the genus-one Green function
G(z|\tau)
in the co-moving coordinates
z=x+y\tau
for
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
first introduced in section
5.5.1
and given by (
5.5.14
),

\displaystyle G(x+y\tau|\tau)=-\ln\left|{\vartheta_{1}(x+y\tau|\tau)\over\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}+2\pi\tau_{2}y^{2}

(12.10.1)

For
\tau_{2}\to\infty
and fixed
x
and
|y|<\tfrac{1}{2}
, the Green function behaves as follows,

\displaystyle G(x+y\tau|\tau)\approx 2\pi\tau_{2}(y^{2}-|y|)

(12.10.2)

which diverges as
\tau_{2}\to\infty
.

The analytic continuation in
s,t,u
of the integrals over moduli now produces double and additional simple poles at all positive integers, as well as branch cuts in
s,t,u
that originate at every non-negative integer, including zero.
These singularities are fully expected on physical grounds. The poles produce mass renormalization and non-zero decay widths of massive string states, while the branch cuts correspond to the decay of a single string into a two-string state. The presence of the branch cuts implies that the string amplitudes
{\cal A}
do not admit a convergent low energy Taylor expansion. We now review the derivation of the branch cuts that arise in the low energy expansion for the case of one-loop amplitudes.

12.10.1

Partitioning the genus-one moduli space

To proceed with the analysis of the branch cuts produced in the low energy limit, we partition the moduli space
{\cal M}_{1}
of genus-one Riemann surfaces into a region near the cusp
{\cal M}_{R}
and its complement
{\cal M}_{L}
, as illustrated in the Figure
14
, and given as follows for
L>1
,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{1}\cap\{\tau_{2}>L\}\hskip 72.26999pt{\cal M}_{L}\cap{\cal M}_{R}=\emptyset

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{1}\cap\{\tau_{2}\leq L\}\hskip 72.26999pt{\cal M}_{L}\cup{\cal M}_{R}={\cal M}_{1}

(12.10.3)

0
-{1\over 2}
{1\over 2}
-1
1
{\cal M}_{L}={\cal M}_{1}\cap\{\tau_{2}<L\}
{\cal M}_{R}={\cal M}_{1}\cap\{\tau_{2}>L\}
{\mathbb{R}}
{\cal H}_{1}
i
iL
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
i\infty

Figure 14
:
Partition of the genus-one moduli space
{\cal M}_{1}
into
{\cal M}_{R}
and
{\cal M}_{L}
.

We shall think of
L
as a free parameter, but in practice take
L\gg 1
so that
{\cal M}_{R}
is a small neighborhood of the cusp. The amplitude
{\cal A}^{(1)}
is a sum of the corresponding integrals,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})={\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})+{\cal A}^{(1)}_{R}(s_{ij})

(12.10.4)

where

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L,R}(s_{ij})={\pi\over 16}\int_{{\cal M}_{L,R}}{|d\tau|^{2}\over({\rm Im\,}\tau)^{2}}\,{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)

(12.10.5)

The region
{\cal M}_{L}
is compact and the integrand
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
admits a uniform low energy expansion in the region of convergence
|s|,|t|,|u|<1
. Therefore, the resulting contribution
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})
will be analytic in
s,t,u
but dependent on
L
.

The region
{\cal M}_{R}
is not compact and
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
cannot be handled with a uniformly convergent low energy expansion. Instead we need a treatment of this contribution that is exact up to contributions exponentially suppressed in
L
. The resulting
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{R}(s_{ij})
will exhibit branch cuts in
s,t,u
and its
L
-dependence will cancel the
L
-dependence of
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})
.

12.10.2

Integrals involving Eisenstein series and modular graph functions

To carry out the integrations over the fundamental domain of the partial amplitudes
{\cal B}^{(1)}_{(p,q)}(\tau)
, one invariably encounters integrals of Eisenstein series, products of Eisenstein series, and other modular graph functions, over the truncated fundamental domain
{\cal M}_{L}
. These integrals are of interest in their own right and we dedicate this subsection to deriving some general results and applying these results to the simplest cases, and then close by quoting the integral of the product of three Eisenstein series from Zagier’s work. We begin with the following straightforward application of Stokes’s theorem to an arbitrary modular graph function
{\cal C}
of weight
(0,0)
, or to an arbitrary modular graph form
{\cal C}^{+}
of weight
(0,2)
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{L}}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,\Delta{\cal C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\tau_{1}\,\partial_{\tau_{2}}{\cal C}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big|_{\tau_{2}=L}

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{L}}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,\nabla{\cal C}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\tau_{1}\,{\cal C}^{+}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Big|_{\tau_{2}=L}

(12.10.6)

where the result on the right side may be read off from the constant Fourier mode part of
{\cal C}
and
{\cal C}^{+}
. The result may be readily used to evaluate the integral of a non-holomorphic Eisenstein series or products thereof. It turns out that the simplest expressions are obtained in terms of the normalized Riemann zeta function
\zeta^{*}(s)
and the normalized non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E^{*}_{s}(\tau)
, defined by,

\displaystyle\zeta^{*}(s)={\Gamma(s/2)\zeta(s)\over\pi^{{s\over 2}}}\hskip 72.26999ptE_{s}^{*}(\tau)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Gamma(s)E_{s}(\tau)

(12.10.7)

The normalization guarantees the functional equations
\zeta^{*}(1-s)=\zeta^{*}(s)
and
E^{*}_{1-s}(\tau)=E^{*}_{s}(\tau)
, in addition to the following simple Fourier expansion,

\displaystyle E^{*}_{s}(\tau)=\zeta^{*}(2s)\,\tau_{2}^{s}+\zeta^{*}(2s-1)\,\tau_{2}^{1-s}+{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}})

(12.10.8)

In terms of these functions, we have the following result.

Lemma 12.1

The following integrals involving Eisenstein series are given, up to exponentially decaying terms
{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}})
at the cusp, by,

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{L}}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,E_{s}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x=s,1-s}\zeta^{*}(2x){L^{x-1}\over x-1}

(12.10.9)

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{L}}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,E_{s}^{*}E_{t}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x=s,1-s}\,\sum_{y=t,1-t}\zeta^{*}(2x)\zeta^{*}(2y){L^{x+y-1}\over x+y-1}

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{L}}{d^{2}\tau\over\tau_{2}^{2}}\,E_{s}^{*}E_{t}^{*}E^{*}_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta^{*}(w-1)\zeta^{*}(w-2s)\zeta^{*}(w-2t)\zeta^{*}(w-2u)

\displaystyle+\sum_{x=s,1-s}\,\sum_{y=t,1-t}\sum_{z=u,1-u}\zeta^{*}(2x)\zeta^{*}(2y)\zeta^{*}(2z){L^{x+y+z-1}\over x+y+z-1}

where
w=s+t+u
in the last line.

The first line in Lemma
12.1
may be proven by substituting
E_{s}^{*}=\Delta E_{s}^{*}/s(s-1)
in the integrand and then using the first line on (
12.10.2
). The second line in Lemma
12.1
may be proven by expressing the combination
\Delta E_{s}^{*}\,E^{*}_{t}-E_{s}^{*}\Delta E^{*}_{t}
in two different ways,

\displaystyle\Delta E_{s}^{*}\,E^{*}_{t}-E_{s}^{*}\Delta E^{*}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(s(s-1)-t(t-1)\Big)E^{*}_{s}E^{*}_{t}

(12.10.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla\left({E^{*}_{s}\bar{\nabla}E_{t}^{*}-E^{*}_{t}\bar{\nabla}E_{s}^{*}\over 2\tau_{2}^{2}}\right)+\bar{\nabla}\left({E^{*}_{s}\nabla E_{t}^{*}-E^{*}_{t}\nabla E_{s}^{*}\over 2\tau_{2}^{2}}\right)

and then using the expression on the right side of the first line to eliminate
E_{s}^{*}E_{t}^{*}
from the integrand of
12.1
in favor of the total derivative terms in the second line. Finally, to integrate the total derivative terms, we use the second line in (
12.10.2
) and its complex conjugate relation along with the asymptotics (
12.10.8
) of the Eisenstein series. The third line in Lemma
12.1
was proven by Zagier using different methods, as will be indicated in the bibliographical notes.

12.10.3

The non-analytic contribution
{\cal A}_{R}^{(1)}

To evaluate
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
for
\tau\in{\cal M}_{R}
, we cannot use (
12.8.1
) since this expansion is not uniformly convergent throughout
{\cal M}_{R}
. Instead, our starting point is the original expression in (
12.5
), which we shall evaluate exactly up to terms that are exponentially suppressed in
L
. To do so, we use co-moving coordinates,
z_{i}=x_{i}+y_{i}\tau
with
i=1,2,3,4
and partition the integration region
\Sigma^{4}
into the six possible orderings of
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}
up to cyclic permutations. Doing so allows us to decompose
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{R}(s_{ij})
into a sum of six more elementary contributions, which are pairwise equal to one another,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}_{R}(s_{ij})=2{\cal A}_{*}(L;s,t)+2{\cal A}_{*}(L;t,u)+2{\cal A}_{*}(L;u,s)\hskip 36.135pts+t+u=0

(12.10.11)

The function
{\cal A}_{*}(L;s,t)
may be computed exactly, up to exponentially suppressed terms,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{*}(L;s,t)=\mathfrak{C}(s,t;0)\,\ln(-4\pi Ls)+{\partial\over\partial\varepsilon}\mathfrak{C}(s,t;\varepsilon)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}+{\cal O}(e^{-2\pi L})

(12.10.12)

where
\mathfrak{C}
is conveniently expressed as the sum of two integral representations,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(s,t;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{8\pi s^{4}\over\Gamma(3+\varepsilon)}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1}dy\,x^{5+2\varepsilon}y^{2+\varepsilon}(1-y)^{2+\varepsilon}W(s,-sxy+t(1-x))

(12.10.13)

\displaystyle-4\pi s^{7}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}s^{k}t^{k}\int_{0}^{1}\!dx\,{x^{k+3+\varepsilon}(1-x)^{k+3+\varepsilon}\over 2\Gamma(k+4+\varepsilon)\,k!}\left[{\partial^{k}\over\partial\mu^{k}}W(s,\mu-sx)\Big|_{\mu=0}\right]^{2}\qquad

The function
W
is closely related to the Virasoro-Shapiro amplitude,

\displaystyle W(s,t)={1\over stu}\left({\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(1-t)\Gamma(1-u)\over\Gamma(1+s)\Gamma(1+t)\Gamma(1+u)}-1\right)\hskip 72.26999ptu=-s-t

(12.10.14)

Although the above expression may look daunting at first, it analytical structure is manifest. The function
W(s,t)
is meromorphic throughout
s,t\in{\mathbb{C}}
, holomorphic in discs of unit radius near the origin, and has only simple poles at positive integer values of
s,t,u
, and thus admits a Taylor expansion in
s,t
. Substituting this Taylor expansion into the integrals defining
\mathfrak{C}
, we see that the integrals preserve the holomorphicity in
s,t,u
near the origin, so that
\mathfrak{C}
itself has a convergent Taylor expansion near the origin, which starts at order
s^{4}
. To this lowest order we have,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(s,t;\varepsilon)=-16\pi\zeta(3)s^{4}{\Gamma(3+\varepsilon)\over\Gamma(7+2\varepsilon)}+{\cal O}(s^{5},s^{4}t)

(12.10.15)

The coefficients of the expansion to all orders inherit contributions linear and bilinear in odd zeta-values from the expansion of the Virasoro-Shapiro amplitude, with rational coefficients in the coefficient
\mathfrak{C}(s,t;0)
of the
\ln(-4\pi Ls)
term, and additional harmonic sums in the
\partial_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{C}(s,t;\varepsilon)\big|_{\varepsilon=0}
analytic contribution to
{\cal A}_{*}(L;s,t)
. The discontinuity of
\ln(-4\pi Ls)
near the origin reproduces the square of the tree-level amplitude by unitarity.

12.10.4

The analytic contribution
{\cal A}_{L}^{(1)}

The contribution
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})
is analytic in
s,t,u
near the origin and we may use the expansion of
{\cal B}^{(1)}(s,t,u|\tau)
in terms of modular graph functions of (
12.8.1
) to evaluate
{\cal A}_{L}^{(1)}(s_{ij})
order by order in powers of
s,t,u
,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}{\cal A}_{(p,q)}^{(1)}\,{\sigma_{2}^{p}\sigma_{3}^{q}\over p!\,q!}

(12.10.16)

Since the non-analytic part
{\cal A}_{R}^{(1)}(s_{ij})
starts contributing to
{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})
only at order
s^{4}
, all its lower orders are given entirely by
{\cal A}^{(1)}_{L}(s_{ij})
. The corresponding amplitudes are readily evaluated, and we find,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(0,0)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle={\pi\over 3}

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(0,1)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle={\pi\over 9}\zeta(3)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(1,0)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(1,1)}^{(1)}

\displaystyle={29\pi\over 540}\zeta(5)

(12.10.17)

The remaining coefficients at weight four are given as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(2,0)}^{(1)}={4\pi\zeta(3)\over 45}\left[\ln(2L)+{\zeta^{\prime}(4)\over\zeta(4)}-{\zeta^{\prime}(3)\over\zeta(3)}-{1\over 4}\right]

(12.10.18)

12.10.5

Assembling analytic and non-analytic contributions

Assembling the contributions to
{\cal A}_{L}^{(1)}
in (
12.10.18
) and to
{\cal A}_{R}^{(1)}
in (
12.10.15
) gives
{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})
, the total amplitude, to order
{\cal O}(s_{ij}^{4})
included. We see explicitly that all
L
-dependence cancels as required for the consistency of the calculation. It is instructive to rearrange the total amplitude as the sum
of “analytic” and “non-analytic” pieces,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(1)}(s_{ij})=2\pi\Big({\cal A}_{L}^{(1)}(L;s_{ij})+{\cal A}_{R}^{(1)}(L;s_{ij})\Big)={\cal A}_{{\rm an}}(s_{ij})+{\cal A}_{{\rm non-an}}(s_{ij})

(12.10.19)

The reason for the quotation marks on analytic and non-analytic is that the non-analytic piece actually contains also analytic contributions, so that the nomenclature is natural and suggestive but not entirely precise.
The “analytic” piece
{\cal A}_{{\rm an}}(s_{ij})
is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{{\rm an}}(s_{ij})={2\pi^{2}\over 3}\left(1+{\zeta(3)\sigma_{3}\over 3}+{\cal O}(s_{ij}^{5})\right)

(12.10.20)

and the “non-analytic” piece
{\cal A}_{{\rm non-an}}(s_{ij})
has the form,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{{\rm non-an}}(s_{ij})={2\pi^{2}\over 3}\left({\cal A}_{{\rm sugra}}+{\cal A}_{4}+{\cal O}(s_{ij}^{5})\right)

(12.10.21)

The lowest-order term
{\cal A}_{{\rm sugra}}
is a regularized version of the ten-dimensional one-loop supergravity amplitude. The contribution
{\cal A}_{4}
is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{4}={4s^{4}\over 15}\,\zeta(3)\left(-\ln(-2\pi s)+{\zeta^{\prime}(4)\over\zeta(4)}-{\zeta^{\prime}(3)\over\zeta(3)}-\gamma+{63\over 20}\right)+\hbox{ cycl }(s,t,u)

(12.10.22)

where
\gamma
is the Euler constant. The discontinuity of
{\cal A}_{{\rm non-an}}(s_{ij})
, namely the coefficient of the
\ln(-2\pi s)
terms in (
12.10.22
), reproduce those obtained in
[
93
]
.

12.11

Integration over the genus-two moduli space

The function
{\cal B}^{(2)}_{(1,0)}(\Omega)
needed to obtain the coefficient of the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
interaction was found to be independent of moduli
\Omega
in subsection
12.9.1
. Thus, its integral over moduli space
{\cal M}_{2}
is given by the volume of
{\cal M}_{2}
with the Siegel metric on the Siegel upper half space,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\sum_{I,J,K,L}Y^{-1}_{IJ}\,Y^{-1}_{KL}\,d\bar{\Omega}_{JK}\,d\Omega_{IL}

(12.11.1)

of constant negative curvature. The volume form
d\mu_{2}
and the value of the volume, computed by Siegel, are given by,

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal M}_{2}}d\mu_{2}={8\pi^{3}\over 270}\hskip 72.26999ptd\mu_{2}={|d\Omega_{11}\wedge d\Omega_{12}\wedge d\Omega_{22}|^{2}\over({\rm det\,}{\rm Im\,}\Omega)^{3}}

(12.11.2)

As a result, the low energy effective interaction to this order is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{(2)}_{(1,0)}={4\pi^{4}\over 270}(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2}){\cal R}^{4}

(12.11.3)

The function
{\cal B}^{(2)}_{(0,1)}(\Omega)
is proportional to the Kawazumi-Zhang invariant
\varphi
. Its integral over
{\cal M}_{2}
is convergent and yields the contribution
{\cal A}^{(2)}_{(0,1)}
to the low energy effective action. Evaluation of the integral is made possible by the fact that
\varphi
satisfies an inhomogeneous Laplace eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle(\Delta-5)\varphi=-2\pi\delta_{SN}

(12.11.4)

where
\Delta
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the Siegel upper half space for the Siegel metric,

\displaystyle\Delta=\sum_{I,J,K,L}4\,Y_{IK}\,Y_{JL}\bar{\partial}_{IJ}\partial_{KL}\hskip 72.26999pt\partial_{IJ}={1\over 2}(1+\delta_{IJ}){\partial\over\partial\Omega_{IJ}}

(12.11.5)

and
\delta_{SN}
is the Dirac
\delta
-function supported on the separating degeneration node.
The coefficient
{\cal B}_{(0,1)}^{(2)}
is then given by the integral of the Kawazumi-Zhang invariant over
{\cal M}_{2}
,

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{(0,1)}^{(2)}=\pi\int_{{\cal M}_{2}}d\mu_{2}\,\varphi={\pi\over 5}\int_{{\cal M}_{2}}d\mu_{2}\Big(\Delta\varphi+2\pi\delta_{SN}\Big)={2\pi^{3}\over 45}

(12.11.6)

The integral may be evaluated using Stokes’s theorem and the asymptotic behavior for the Kawazumi-Zhang invariant, and we find,

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{(0,1)}^{(2)}={2\pi^{3}\over 45}(s^{3}+t^{3}+u^{3}){\cal R}^{4}

(12.11.7)

For possible verifications to genus-three order, we refer to the references of this section.

Bibliographical notes

Classic books by Green, Schwarz, and Witten
[
169
,
170
]
and by Polchinski
[
171
,
172
]
present comprehensive perspectives on string theory. A pedagogical introduction, aimed at undergraduate and graduate students, is given in the book by Zwiebach
[
173
]
. Other accounts may be found in the books by Johnson
[
174
]
, by Becker, Becker, and Schwarz
[
175
]
, by Blumenhagen, Lüst, and Theissen
[
176
]
, and by Kiritsis
[
177
]
. A beautiful discussion of the connection between modular/automorphic forms and string amplitudes can be found in the book by Fleig, Gustafsson, Kleinschmidt, and Persson
[
24
]
.

The early development of string theory was dominated by the study of string amplitudes. The Ramond and Neveu-Schwarz sectors of the RNS formulation were introduced in
[
178
]
and
[
179
]
, respectively. The different spin structure sectors and the Gliozzi-Scherk-Olive (GSO) projection onto supersymmetric string spectra were introduced in
[
180
]
.
The Polyakov formulation of string theory, along with many other key problems in modern theoretical physics, is presented in the book by Polyakov
[
181
]
. Friedan’s renormalization of the non-linear
\sigma
-model and the relation between Einstein’s equations and conformal invariance may be found in
[
182
]
. Generalizations with worldsheet fermions are given in
[
183
]
, and including torsion in
[
184
]
and the dilaton in
[
185
]
. The
\alpha^{\prime}
expansion of the effective action including the metric, anti-symmetric tensor field, and dilaton is presented in detail in the book of Princeton Institute for Advanced Study lecture notes
[
186
]
.

The standard treatment of the decoupling of negative norm states for both the bosonic and superstrings is clearly reviewed in
[
169
]
. The modern BRST-based approach to the covariant quantization of superstrings in the RNS formulation was developed in
[
51
]
, and reviewed in
[
169
,
170
]
and
[
171
,
172
]
. The structure of gauge and gravitational anomalies, relevant to supergravity and string theories in various dimensions, was obtained in
[
187
]
. The cancellation of anomalies in the Type I theory with gauge group
SO(32)
was famously discovered in
[
188
]
. Useful lecture notes on anomalies may be found in
[
189
]
.

Reviews of superstring perturbation theory may be found in
[
190
,
191
]
for the RNS formulation and in
[
192
]
for the pure spinor formulation. Lectures accessible to a more mathematically oriented readership are in
[
186
]
, while an elaboration of the mathematical underpinning of the RNS formulation is provided in
[
193
,
194
]
. A broad overview of the current status of superstring perturbation theory, and open problems, is given in
[
195
]
, which contains an extensive bibliography.

The tree-level and one-loop four graviton amplitudes were derived in the original papers in which the Type II
[
196
]
and Heterotic strings
[
197
,
198
]
were discovered. The existence of the analytic continuation of the one-loop four graviton amplitude was shown in
[
199
,
200
]
.

The fact that the low energy expansion of string theory leads to
{\cal N}=4
super-Yang-Mills theory and
{\cal N}=8
supergravity in four space-time dimensions was discovered in
[
201
]
. The systematic analysis of the low energy effective interactions induced by the tree-level four-graviton amplitude was initiated in
[
202
,
203
]
,
while the effects at genus-one were obtained in
[
204
,
205
,
93
]
. The low energy expansion, up to order
D^{8}{\cal R}^{4}
, including the logarithmic branch cuts and the transcendentality properties of the amplitude discussed in section
12.7.2
were obtained in
[
206
]
.

The original Rankin-Selberg method for integrating cusp forms over the fundamental domain for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

was developed in
[

,

]
, and was extended to automorphic functions of non-rapid decay in

]
. It was applied to string amplitudes in
[

]
and subsequent works

,

]
. The proof of the last integral identity in Lemma
12.1
is in
[

]
, while methods to carry out integrals of modular graph functions may be found in
[

]
and references therein.

Considerable progress has been made recently towards evaluating tree-level and genus-one string amplitudes with arbitrary numbers of external states in
[

,
215
,
216
,
217
,
218
,
219
]
and references therein. The role of motivic and multiple zeta-values played in the form of the amplitudes was examined in

,
221
,
222
]
.
Genus-one amplitudes have also recently been reproduced from
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory by considering a flat-space limit of
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}

[
223
]
.

For genus-two amplitudes, the measure on supermoduli space was derived in the RNS formulation in
[
224
,
225
,
226
]
and reproduced using algebraic-geometric methods in
[
227
]
. The full amplitude for four gravitons was obtained in
[

in the RNS formulation, and extended to include external fermion states in
[

]
using the pure spinor formulation. The overall normalization of the amplitude was obtained in
[

,

.

Recently, the genus-two amplitudes with five massless states were constructed in
[

,

]
using an amalgam of methods from the RNS and pure spinor formulations and. For external NS boson states and even spin structure, these results were confirmed from first principles in the RNS formulation in
[

]
.

The generalization of modular graph functions to genus two and higher genus was developed in
[
124
]
while the relation between the coefficient of the
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
term and the invariant of Kawazumi
[
236
]
and Zhang
[
237
]
was identified in
[

]
and used to derive the corresponding differential equation in
[

]
. Degenerations of genus-two modular forms naturally produce non-holomorphic versions of Jacobi forms, systematic treatment of which may be found in the book by Eichler and Zagier
[

]
. A
\vartheta
-lift representation of the genus-two Kawazumi-Zhang invariant was obtained in
[

]
. For the derivation of the results in section

we refer to
[

]
.

For amplitudes at genus three and beyond, no first principle derivations are available at the time of this writing.
A proposal for the measure on supermoduli space in the RNS formulation was advanced in
[

]
,
building on some earlier unsuccessful attempts in
[

]
. The proposal of
[

]
was critiqued, however, in the appendix of
[

]
. The leading low energy effective interaction in Type II string theory was obtained using the pure spinor formulation in
[
245
]
. The corresponding expression for the amplitude, however, diverges beyond the leading low energy limit so that no independent checks on the unitarity of the amplitude is available. A proposal for the full genus-three four graviton amplitude has been advanced in
[
