]
, but details of the derivation are not yet available. Proposals for the measure on supermoduli space in the RNS formulation at genus four may be found in
[
247
,
248
]
.

13

Toroidal compactification

In this section, we discuss the modular properties of quantum field theory of scalar fields that take values in a
d
-dimensional torus with a flat metric and constant anti-symmetric tensor. The problem is of great interest in quantum field theory and string theory in view of the fact that such toroidal compactifications admit solutions using free field theory methods on the worldsheet, preserve Poincaré supersymmetries, and may be used to relate different perturbative string theories via T-duality. Toroidal compactifications produce large duality groups that generalize the modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
considered thus far.

13.1

Conformal field theory on flat manifolds

In section
5
we considered the functional integral for a single free scalar field
\varphi
that takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}
. The situation is readily generalized to the case of
d
free scalar fields taking values in a flat Euclidean
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. As discussed in section
12
, the generalization to
d
scalar fields taking values in (the local coordinate systems of) an arbitrary space-time manifold in general leads to fully interacting quantum field theories on the worldsheet that cannot be solved exactly.

There is one class of theories, however, where an exact quantization may be obtained, namely when all the fields
G,B,\Phi
are constant, and the manifold
M
is flat. Although flat,
M
may have non-trivial topology as in the case of a
d
-dimensional torus
M=T_{d}
, which is the case we shall consider here. Constant
\Phi
gives rise to a term proportional to the Euler number of
\Sigma
and weighs the functional integral by a power of the string coupling which is independent on the quantum field
X
. Finally, the term involving
B_{\mu\nu}
is a topological term when
B
is constant. It does not contribute when the topology of
M
is trivial, but when
M
is a torus, it does have very interesting physical effects which we shall now examine. For constant
G,B,\Phi
, the
X
-dependent part of the action thus reduces to,

\displaystyle I[X,g]={1\over 4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{g}\,\Big(g^{mn}G_{\mu\nu}-i\,\varepsilon^{mn}B_{\mu\nu}\Big)\partial_{m}X^{\mu}\partial_{n}X^{\nu}

(13.1.1)

Henceforth, we shall restrict to the case where the worldsheet surface is a torus of modulus
\tau
, so that
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}+\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
. We shall choose a system of complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
on
\Sigma
in which the metric
g
takes the form,

\displaystyle g_{mn}\,d\xi^{m}d\xi^{n}={|dz|^{2}\over\tau_{2}}

(13.1.2)

or in components we have
g_{zz}=g_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}=0
and
g_{z\bar{z}}=1/(2\tau_{2})
. Furthermore, the space-time torus
T_{d}
is given by
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L
for some
d
-dimensional lattice
L
.

13.2

Lattices and tori of dimension
d

A lattice
L
of rank
d
, also referred to as a lattice of dimension
d
, may be represented in terms of a basis of
d
vectors
v_{1},\cdots,v_{d}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle L=\Big\{\ell=\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}v_{i}~~n_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big\}

(13.2.1)

It will be useful to view the assignment of basis vectors as obtained by a
GL(d,{\mathbb{R}})
transformation applied to a standard basis of
d
vectors, such as for example
v_{1}^{0}=(1,0,\cdots,0)
,
v_{2}^{0}=(0,1,0,\cdots,0),\cdots,v_{n}^{0}=(0,\cdots,0,1)
. Decomposing
GL(d,{\mathbb{R}})=SL(d,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, the factor
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
corresponds to overall rescaling of the lattice. An arbitrary set of
d
vectors
v_{i}^{\prime}\in L
for
i=1,\cdots,d
may be decomposed in the basis
v_{i}
,

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}M_{ij}v_{j}\hskip 72.26999ptM_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.2.2)

and generates a sub-lattice
L^{\prime}\subset L
. Lattices may be viewed as Abelian groups so that
L^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
L
. The index of
L^{\prime}
as a subgroup of
L
is given as follows,

\displaystyle[L:L^{\prime}]=|{\rm det\,}M|

(13.2.3)

Equivalently, the unit lattice cell of
L^{\prime}
contains
|{\rm det\,}M|
copies of the unit lattice cell of
L
. In the special case where
{\rm det\,}M=1
, we have
L^{\prime}=L
and therefore
M
is an automorphism of
L
. The group of all automorphisms of
L
is
SL(d,{\mathbb{Z}})
, thereby generalizing to
d
dimensions the result familiar from the two-dimensional torus. When
{\rm det\,}M=-1
the lattice
L^{\prime}
is the mirror image of
L
, reversing the orientation of the lattice.

Introducing the equivalence relation between two lattices that are related to one another by an overall rescaling in
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, an overall rotation in
SO(d)
, and a lattice automorphism in
SL(d,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we may identify the space of all inequivalent lattices as follows,

\displaystyle\hbox{space of inequivalent lattices }=SL(d,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash SL(d,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(d,{\mathbb{R}})

(13.2.4)

In the case of a two-dimensional lattice
L
with
d=2
, we may identify
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2)
with the Poincaré upper half-plane
{\cal H}
and the entire coset
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2,{\mathbb{R}})
with the moduli space of toroidal Riemann surfaces. The dynamics of point particles propagating on the torus
T_{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L
is sensitive to the lattice geometry of this quotient. We shall see below that the dynamics of strings propagating on the torus
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L
is sensitive to a different equivalence class of lattices, which is “smaller” than
SL(d,{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash SL(d,{\mathbb{R}})
thanks to the quintessentially string-theoretic phenomenon of
T
-duality.

13.3

Fields taking values in a torus

We begin by reviewing the construction of the space of
d
scalar fields
X(z)
that take values in the torus
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L
and are functions on a worldsheet
\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
with the topology of a two-dimensional torus, whose complex structure is given by its modulus
\tau\in{\cal H}
so that
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\tau{\mathbb{Z}}
. The space of maps,

\displaystyle X:\,\Sigma={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda\,\to\,T_{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L

(13.3.1)

is disconnected and may be decomposed into the union of an infinite number of connected components which are labelled by two copies of the lattice
L
. In a sector labelled by a pair
(\ell_{A},\ell_{B})\in L\times L
, the field
X^{\mu}
satisfies the following monodromy conditions,

\displaystyle X(z+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X(z)+\ell_{A}

\displaystyle X(z+\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X(z)+\ell_{B}\hskip 72.26999pt\ell_{A},\ell_{B}\in L

(13.3.2)

The field
X
may be decomposed into a purely periodic part
Y
, which satisfies,

\displaystyle Y(z+1)=Y(z+\tau)=Y(z)

(13.3.3)

and a special solution to the monodromy conditions. The latter is arbitrary but it will be convenient to choose it linear in
z
and
\bar{z}
. Since the field
X
and the vectors
\ell_{A},\ell_{B}
are real-valued, we have the following decomposition,

\displaystyle X_{\ell_{A},\ell_{B}}(z)=Y(z)+z\,{\ell_{B}-\bar{\tau}\ell_{A}\over\tau-\bar{\tau}}-\bar{z}\,{\ell_{B}-\tau\ell_{A}\over\tau-\bar{\tau}}

(13.3.4)

Such a configuration is sometimes referred to as a worldsheet instanton. The lattice elements
\ell_{A},\ell_{B}
are related to the momentum and the winding modes of strings on
T_{d}
, as we shall clarify below. The integration over all
X:\Sigma\to T_{d}
decomposes into a sum over sectors labelled by
L\times L
. The functional integral takes the following form,

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm maps}(\Sigma\to T_{d})}DXe^{-I[X,g]}={\rm Vol}(T_{d})\sum_{\ell_{A},\ell_{B}\in L}\int_{{\rm maps}(\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d})}D^{\prime}Y\,e^{-I[X_{\ell_{A},\ell_{B}},g]}

(13.3.5)

For given worldsheet metric
g
, the measures
DX
and
D^{\prime}Y
are evaluated using the norm of (
12.2.2
).
The factor
{\rm Vol}(T_{d})
arises from the normalizable zero mode of the field
Y
, and the integration
D^{\prime}Y
is over all non-zero modes, i.e. all functions
Y
orthogonal to constants. The contribution to the action of
Y
decouples from the contribution of the momentum and winding modes, which may be evaluated explicitly,

\displaystyle I[X_{\ell_{A},\ell_{B}},g]=I[Y,g]+{1\over 4\pi\alpha^{\prime}\tau_{2}}(\ell^{\mu}_{B}-\bar{\tau}\ell^{\mu}_{A})(\ell^{\nu}_{B}-\tau\ell^{\nu}_{A})(G_{\mu\nu}+B_{\mu\nu})

(13.3.6)

The integral over
Y
factorizes, the dependence on
B_{\mu\nu}
cancels out in this integral and reduces to the functional integrals computed earlier in section
5
. The remaining factor is given by the following lattice sum,

\displaystyle Z(L,\tau)={\rm Vol}(T_{d})\sum_{\ell_{A},\ell_{B}\in L}\exp\left\{-{1\over 4\pi\alpha^{\prime}\tau_{2}}(\ell^{\mu}_{B}-\bar{\tau}\ell^{\mu}_{A})(\ell^{\nu}_{B}-\tau\ell^{\nu}_{A})(G_{\mu\nu}+B_{\mu\nu})\right\}

(13.3.7)

The dependence on
B_{\mu\nu}
reduces to
-iB_{\mu\nu}\,\ell^{\mu}_{A}\,\ell^{\nu}_{B}/(2\pi\alpha^{\prime})
and is independent of
\tau
, as is expected for a topological term. Before investigating the properties for a general torus
T_{d}
, we first look at the much simpler case of
d=1
.

13.4

T-duality on a circle

In this subsection, we study the simplest case of toroidal compactification when
d=1
and exhibit the phenomenon of
T
-duality for a circle. We take
G=1
, and introduce the lattice
L
associated with a circle of radius
R
, namely
L=2\pi R\,{\mathbb{Z}}
. We label the monodromy vectors
\ell_{A}
and
\ell_{B}
, which are one-dimensional in this case, as follows,

\displaystyle\ell_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi R\,n

\displaystyle\ell_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi R\,m\hskip 72.26999ptm,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.4.1)

The sum over momentum and winding modes then simplifies to,

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)=\sqrt{R^{2}\over\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\exp\left\{-{\pi R^{2}\over\alpha^{\prime}\tau_{2}}|m-\tau n|^{2}\right\}

(13.4.2)

The prefactor in
\sqrt{R^{2}/\alpha^{\prime}}
arises from the zero mode of the field
Y
which, for a circle of radius
R
, gives a finite contribution. The above sum
Z(R,\tau)
is a rather familiar looking object. To exhibit
T
-duality, we perform a Poisson resummation on both
m,n
. To do so we compute the Fourier transform in both variables,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}dm\,dn\,e^{-2\pi i(mx+ny)}\,\exp\left\{-{\pi R^{2}\over\alpha^{\prime}\tau_{2}}|m-\tau n|^{2}\right\}={\alpha^{\prime}\over R^{2}}\,\exp\left\{-{\pi\alpha^{\prime}\over R^{2}\tau_{2}}|y+\tau x|^{2}\right\}

(13.4.3)

so that Poisson resummation gives us,

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}\over R^{2}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\exp\left\{-{\pi\alpha^{\prime}\over R^{2}\tau_{2}}|m+\tau n|^{2}\right\}

(13.4.4)

Noticing that Poisson resummation has inverted the dependence on
R^{2}/\alpha^{\prime}
, we have,

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)=Z\left({\alpha^{\prime}\over R},\tau\right)

(13.4.5)

Thus, a free bosonic string on a circle of radius
R
has exactly the same partition function as a free bosonic string on a circle of radius
\alpha^{\prime}/R
. While we have proven this above for the free string on a genus-one worldsheet only, the effect actually is valid on surfaces of arbitrary genus, and thus holds for fully interacting string theory. The effect is referred to as
T
-duality.

We may organize the double sum in a slightly different way which makes the decomposition into momentum and winding modes transparent. Performing a Poisson resummation on (
13.4.2
) in
m
only, we obtain the representation,

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)=(\tau_{2})^{1\over 2}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{2\pi i\tau p_{L}^{2}}\,e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}p_{R}^{2}}

(13.4.6)

where the left and right momenta are given in terms of the integers
m,n
by,

\displaystyle p_{L}={1\over 2}\left({\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\over R}m+{R\over\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}n\right)

\displaystyle p_{L}+p_{R}={\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\over R}m

\displaystyle p_{R}={1\over 2}\left({\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\over R}m-{R\over\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}n\right)

\displaystyle p_{L}-p_{R}={R\over\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}n

(13.4.7)

The lattice points
p_{L}+p_{R}\in\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}/R\,{\mathbb{Z}}
are clearly associated with the total momentum of the string, while the lattice points
p_{L}-p_{R}\in R/\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\,{\mathbb{Z}}
are associated with the winding modes.

The space of all possible compactifications on the circle is labeled by the radius
R
modulo the identification
R\equiv\alpha^{\prime}/R
, a space we may denote by
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. Clearly, the lattices
L=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}/R\,{\mathbb{Z}}
and
L^{+}=R/\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\,{\mathbb{Z}}
are dual to one another, a fact that is reflected in the relation
(p_{L}+p_{R})(p_{L}-p_{R})=mn\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus, the lattice sum may be written alternatively as,

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)=(\tau_{2})^{1\over 2}\sum_{p\in L}\sum_{w\in L^{+}}e^{\pi i\tau(p+w)^{2}/2}\,e^{-\pi i\bar{\tau}(p-w)^{2}/2}

(13.4.8)

At the radius
R=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
the conformal field theory is self-dual and enjoys an enhanced symmetry to the
SU(2)
Kac-Moody algebra. The radius
R=\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}
is also special in a different sense: it is at this point that the
\mathfrak{c}=1
Virasoro algebra representation of the conformal field theory becomes reducible to two
\mathfrak{c}={1\over 2}
fermions.

T
-duality has several important consequences for string theory, which include,

1.

The fact that (bosonic) string theory on a circle of radius
R
is physically equivalent to a string theory on a circle of radius
\alpha^{\prime}/R
means that we can never physically probe distances smaller than
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Analogous arguments exist for the energy of scattering processes, namely probing a string with energy
E
and with energy
1/(\alpha^{\prime}E)
are physically equivalent. It means that string theory has built in its dynamics a

smallest string length scale
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
.

2.

Actually, the above picture is modified in the case of superstring theories. In Type II string theories, compactification of Type IIA on a circle of radius
R
is physically equivalent to Type IIB theory on a circle of radius
\alpha^{\prime}/R
. In Heterotic string theories, the
E_{8}\times E_{8}
theory is invariant under
R\leftrightarrow\alpha^{\prime}/R
but the
Spin(32)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
theory is exchanged with the theory of open and closed strings Type I.
In both cases,
T
-duality provides a mechanism for a partial unification of the five 10-dimensional superstring theories.

3.

T
-duality extends to string theories on curved manifolds under certain conditions. For example, on Calabi-Yau spaces, it is promoted to mirror symmetry.

13.5

T-duality on a torus
T_{d}

For a general torus
T_{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}/L
with
d\geq 2
new effects arise. We consider the lattice sum of (
13.3.7
) and introduce a basis
\{v_{1}^{\mu},\cdots,v_{d}^{\mu}\}
with
\mu=1,\cdots,d
for the lattice
L
. Generalizing the conventions of (
13.4
) used for the circle, we have,

\displaystyle\ell_{A}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{d}n^{i}\,v_{i}^{\mu}\hskip 72.26999ptn^{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle\ell_{B}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{d}m^{i}\,v_{i}^{\mu}\hskip 72.26999ptm^{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.5.1)

What enters
Z(L,\tau)
is the combination,

\displaystyle{1\over\alpha^{\prime}}(G_{\mu\nu}+B_{\mu\nu})v^{\mu}_{i}v^{\nu}_{j}={\cal G}_{ij}+{\cal B}_{ij}

(13.5.2)

where
{\cal G}_{ij}
and
{\cal B}_{ij}
are respectively symmetric and anti-symmetric under the interchange of the indices
i,j
.
Using matrix notation for
m,n,{\cal G},{\cal B}
, we have,

\displaystyle Z(L,\tau)={\rm Vol}(T^{d})\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\left\{-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}(m+\tau n)^{\dagger}({\cal G}+{\cal B})(m+\tau n)\right\}

(13.5.3)

Since
v_{i}
forms a basis of
L
, the volume of the torus is given by,

\displaystyle{\rm Vol}(T_{d})=({\rm det\,}{\cal G})^{1\over 2}

(13.5.4)

up to some factor of
2\pi
which will be of no interest here.

13.5.1

A first look at
T
-duality

The argument of the exponential may be decomposed as follows,

\displaystyle-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}(m+\tau n)^{\dagger}({\cal G}+{\cal B})(m+\tau n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}(m+\tau_{1}n)^{t}{\cal G}(m+\tau_{1}n)

(13.5.5)

\displaystyle-\pi\tau_{2}n^{t}{\cal G}n-2\pi im^{t}{\cal B}n

As a result, each term in the exponential is invariant under a shift in
{\cal B}
by an arbitrary anti-symmetric matrix with integer entries, or in components,

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{ij}\to{\cal B}_{ij}+b_{ij}\hskip 72.26999ptb_{ij}=-b_{ji}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.5.6)

This invariance is an immediate consequence of the fact that, for constant
B
, the
B
-term is topological and its values on the field configurations for the torus
T_{d}
are quantized. The corresponding symmetry is the analogue of the
T
-transformation
\tau_{1}\to\tau_{1}+1
for
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, and will provide some of the generators of the full
T
-duality group for
T_{d}
.

Next, we wish to obtain the analogue of the
S
-transformation. To do so, we Poisson resum in both
m,n
.
It is helpful to express the quadratic form combining
m,n
into a single column matrix, so that,

\displaystyle-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}(m+\tau n)^{\dagger}({\cal G}+{\cal B})(m+\tau n)=-\pi\left(\begin{matrix}m\cr n\cr\end{matrix}\right)^{t}{\cal M}\left(\begin{matrix}m\cr n\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.7)

where
{\cal M}
is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal M}={1\over\tau_{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}{\cal G}&&\tau_{1}{\cal G}+i\tau_{2}{\cal B}\cr&&\cr\tau_{1}{\cal G}-i\tau_{2}{\cal B}&&(\tau_{1}^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}){\cal G}\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.8)

The inverse matrix may be expressed as follows,

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{-1}={1\over\tau_{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}0&&I\cr&&\cr-I&&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)^{t}\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{\cal G}&&\tau_{1}\tilde{\cal G}+i\tau_{2}\tilde{\cal B}\cr&&\cr\tau_{1}\tilde{\cal G}-i\tau_{2}\tilde{\cal B}&&(\tau_{1}^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2})\tilde{\cal G}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0&&I\cr&&\cr-I&&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.9)

where
\tilde{\cal G}
and
\tilde{\cal B}
are given by the
S
-transformation,

\displaystyle\tilde{\cal G}=\Big({\cal G}+{\cal B}^{t}\,{\cal G}^{-1}\,{\cal B}\Big)^{-1}\hskip 72.26999pt\tilde{\cal B}={\cal G}^{-1}{\cal B}\,\tilde{\cal G}

(13.5.10)

The action of
S^{2}
is obtained by repeating the transformation,

\displaystyle\hat{\cal G}=\Big(\tilde{\cal G}+\tilde{\cal B}^{t}\,\tilde{\cal G}^{-1}\,\tilde{\cal B}\Big)^{-1}\hskip 72.26999pt\hat{\cal B}=\tilde{\cal G}^{-1}\tilde{\cal B}\,\hat{\cal G}

(13.5.11)

and eliminating
\tilde{\cal B}
and
\tilde{\cal G}
. The result is
\hat{\cal B}={\cal B}
and
\hat{\cal G}={\cal G}
, so that the action of
S^{2}
on
{\cal G},{\cal B}
is the identity.
25
25

25

Although in
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
the matrix
S
squares to
S^{2}=-I
, its action on
\tau
reduces to the identity; the above statement is the direct analogue thereof.
We shall show shortly that
S
and the integer translations of
{\cal B}
generate the duality group
SO(d,d,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

13.5.2

Holomorphic block decomposition

As in the case of the scalar field theory valued in the circle, it is instructive to perform a Poisson resummation only on the integers
m
, and expose the holomorphic structure in
\tau
. To do so, we start from the decomposition of the quadratic form given earlier, and we need the following Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}d^{d}x\,e^{-2\pi ix^{t}m}\,\exp\left\{-{\pi\over\tau_{2}}(x+\tau_{1}n)^{t}{\cal G}(x+\tau_{1}n)-2\pi ix^{t}{\cal B}n\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad={(\tau_{2})^{{d\over 2}}\over({\rm det\,}{\cal G})^{1\over 2}}\exp\Big\{2\pi i\tau p_{L}^{2}-2\pi i\bar{\tau}p_{R}^{2}\Big\}

(13.5.12)

where we obtain after minimal simplifications,

\displaystyle 4p_{L}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m+{\cal B}n)^{t}{\cal G}^{-1}(m+{\cal B}n)+n^{t}{\cal G}n+2m^{t}n

\displaystyle 4p_{R}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m+{\cal B}n)^{t}{\cal G}^{-1}(m+{\cal B}n)+n^{t}{\cal G}n-2m^{t}n

(13.5.13)

Note that while the integers
n
were defined with an upper index, as were the old
m
, by contrast the Poisson resummation variable
m
have lower indices. So, it is instructive to write out the above relations in terms of indices,

\displaystyle 4p_{L}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(m_{i}+{\cal B}_{ii^{\prime}}n^{i^{\prime}}\Big)({\cal G}^{-1})^{ij}\Big(m_{j}+{\cal B}_{jj^{\prime}}n^{j^{\prime}}\Big)+n^{i}{\cal G}_{ij}n^{j}+2m_{i}n^{i}

\displaystyle 4p_{R}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(m_{i}+{\cal B}_{ii^{\prime}}n^{i^{\prime}}\Big)({\cal G}^{-1})^{ij}\Big(m_{j}+{\cal B}_{jj^{\prime}}n^{j^{\prime}}\Big)+n^{i}{\cal G}_{ij}n^{j}-2m_{i}n^{i}

In evaluating the partition function, the factor
{\rm Vol}(T^{d})
cancels out, and we find,

\displaystyle Z(L,\tau)=(\tau_{2})^{{d\over 2}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\exp\Big\{2\pi i\tau\,p_{L}^{2}-2\pi i\bar{\tau}\,p_{R}^{2}\Big\}

(13.5.14)

with
p_{L}^{2},p_{R}^{2}
functions of
m,n
as defined above. The invariance under integer shifts of the matrix
{\cal B}
is now manifested by the fact that such a shift may be compensated in the sum by a shift in the integers
m
.

13.5.3

T
-duality in terms of the lattice

The description of the torus with both an arbitrary lattice
L
and an arbitrary metric
G
is in fact redundant. By general linear transformation, we may always make the metric equal to the identity. Geometrically, this is equivalent to expressing the metric
{\cal G}_{ij}
in terms of an orthonormal frame
e_{\mu}{}^{a}
and its inverse
e_{a}{}^{\mu}
by

\displaystyle{\cal G}_{ij}=e_{i}{}^{a}e_{j}{}^{b}\delta_{ab}

(13.5.15)

where
i
are “Einstein indices” and
a
are frame indices. The lattice
L
with identity metric is then generated by the vector
e_{a}{}^{i}
, while the dual lattice
L^{+}
is generated by the dual vector
e_{i}{}^{a}
. Defining also the
B
-field in frame indices,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{ab}={\cal B}_{ij}\,e_{a}{}^{i}e_{b}{}^{j}

(13.5.16)

and defining the lattice and dual lattice vectors,

\displaystyle\mu_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{i}\,e_{a}{}^{i}

\displaystyle\nu^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{i}\,e_{i}{}^{a}

(13.5.17)

we find that
p_{L}^{2}
and
p_{R}^{2}
may be interpreted as square of vectors, respectively given by,

\displaystyle p_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\Big(\mu+\mathfrak{B}\,\nu+\nu\Big)\hskip 72.26999pt\mu\in L^{+}

\displaystyle p_{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\Big(\mu+\mathfrak{B}\,\nu-\nu\Big)\hskip 72.26999pt\nu\in L

(13.5.18)

with,

\displaystyle p_{L}\cdot p_{L}-p_{R}\cdot p_{R}=\mu\cdot\nu=m^{t}n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.5.19)

To parametrize the space of all lattices, we proceed as follows. Given a lattice, it may be deformed continuously provided we maintain the condition
p_{L}\cdot p_{L}-p_{R}\cdot p_{R}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. This condition is invariant under the group
SO(d,d;{\mathbb{R}})
. But rotations under
SO(d)
on both
p_{L}
and
p_{R}
are physically indistinguishable from the original lattice. Therefore, any lattice and its dual may be parametrized by a point in the coset space,

\displaystyle SO(d,d;{\mathbb{R}})/(SO(d;{\mathbb{R}})\times SO(d;{\mathbb{R}}))

(13.5.20)

The dimension of this space is,

\displaystyle{1\over 2}(2d)(2d-1)-2\times{1\over 2}d(d-1)=d^{2}

(13.5.21)

This dimension is easily understood in the original formulation with arbitrary metric
G
and
B
-field in a square torus: together they have
d^{2}
components.

Two different points in
SO(d,d;{\mathbb{R}})/(SO(d;{\mathbb{R}})\times SO(d;{\mathbb{R}}))
may correspond to the same lattice.
We already know that this is the case when
{\cal B}_{ij}
are shifted by integers, and when the
S
transformation is applied to both
{\cal G}
and
{\cal B}
. The shift is realized in terms of an anti-symmetric matrix
K
with integer entries,

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}I+K&-K\cr K&I-K\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{p}_{L}\cr\tilde{p}_{R}\cr\end{matrix}\right)=\gamma\left(\begin{matrix}p_{L}\cr p_{R}\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.22)

whose effect is to shift
{\cal B}\to{\cal B}+2K
. The matrix
\gamma
is easily seen to belong to
SO(d,d;{\mathbb{R}})
, and to be a translation or parabolic element, such that,

\displaystyle\gamma^{n}=\left(\begin{matrix}I+nK&-nK\cr nK&I-nK\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.23)

The
S
-transformation is given by,

\displaystyle S=\left(\begin{matrix}0&I\cr-I&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(13.5.24)

The space of inequivalent tori
T_{d}
from the string point of view are thus parametrized by the double coset space,

\displaystyle SO(d,d;{\mathbb{Z}})\backslash SO(d,d;{\mathbb{R}})/SO(d;{\mathbb{R}})\times SO(d;{\mathbb{R}})

(13.5.25)

In the case of heterotic string, the result extends to include the left-moving 16 dimensional torus, and the duality group is
SO(d,d+16;{\mathbb{Z}})
.

13.6

Rationality and complex multiplication

In section
10.10
we introduced the torus partition function of a two-dimensional conformal field theory, e.g. in (
10.10.7
). A CFT is said to be rational if the sum over characters is finite. In a sense, a rational CFT is one which is exactly solvable, and great effort has been expended towards obtaining a classification of such theories.
In this section, we ask when a
T_{2}
non-linear sigma model is rational.

13.6.1

The case
T_{1}

To begin, let us consider the simpler case of an
S^{1}
non-linear sigma model, with the circle having radius
R
. In this case, the partition function may be computed to be

\displaystyle Z(R,\tau)={1\over|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{{1\over 2}\left({m\over R}+{nR\over 2}\right)^{2}}\bar{q}^{{1\over 2}\left({m\over R}-{nR\over 2}\right)^{2}}

(13.6.1)

This comes from (
13.4.6
) by choosing
\alpha^{\prime}=2
and dressing with a factor of
|\eta(\tau)|^{2}
to account for Virasoro descendants. To address the question of rationality, we must ask when this can be written as a sum over a finite number of characters. It turns out that this is possible if
R^{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Indeed, say that
R^{2}={2k\over\ell}
for
k,\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In this case we can write the partition function as,

\displaystyle Z\left(\sqrt{{2k\over\ell}},\,\tau\right)={1\over|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{{1\over 4k\ell}(m\ell+nk)^{2}}\overline{q}^{{1\over 4k\ell}(m\ell-nk)^{2}}

(13.6.2)

It will be useful to write

\displaystyle m=\mu+2kr\hskip 57.81621ptn=\nu+2\ell s

(13.6.3)

where
\mu\in\{0,\dots,2k-1\}
,
\nu\in\{0,\dots,2\ell-1\}
, and
r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We may then split the sums as

\displaystyle Z\left(\sqrt{{2k\over\ell}},\,\tau\right)={1\over|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{\mu=0}^{2k-1}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\ell-1}\sum_{r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{{1\over 4k\ell}(2k\ell(r+s)+\mu\ell+\nu k)^{2}}\overline{q}^{{1\over 4k\ell}(2k\ell(r-s)+\mu\ell-\nu k)^{2}}

(13.6.4)

We now again change summation variables to,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}=\mu\ell+\nu k\hskip 36.135pt\tilde{\nu}=\mu\ell-\nu k\hskip 36.135pt\rho=r+s\hskip 36.135pt\sigma=r-s

(13.6.5)

By definition we have,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}+\tilde{\nu}\equiv 0\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2\ell)\hskip 57.81621pt\tilde{\mu}-\tilde{\nu}\equiv 0\,\,\,({\rm mod}\,\,2k)

(13.6.6)

Hence we can rewrite the partition function as a finite sum,

\displaystyle Z\left(\sqrt{{2k\over\ell}},\,\tau\right)=\sum^{\prime}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}=0,\dots,2k\ell-1\end{subarray}}\chi_{\tilde{\mu}}(\tau)\overline{\chi}_{\tilde{\nu}}(\bar{\tau})

(13.6.7)

where the primed sum denotes a sum subject to restrictions (
13.6.6
). The result has been written in terms of the characters,

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\mu}}(\tau)={1\over\eta(\tau)}\sum_{\rho\in{\mathbb{Z}}}q^{k\ell\left(\rho+{\tilde{\mu}\over 2k\ell}\right)^{2}}

(13.6.8)

That these are the correct characters for
T_{1}
is confirmed by noting that the compact boson at radius
R^{2}={2k\over\ell}
is equivalent to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
orbifold of the
U(1)_{k}
current algebra theory.

13.6.2

The case
T_{2}

We now proceed to the case of
M=T_{2}
. For the moment we will consider only tori that are trivial products of circles, with radii
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
. Then the CFT factorizes into two
S^{1}
CFTs studied above, and the total CFT is rational if and only if the two components CFTs are. In other words, we must take
R_{1,2}^{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Say concretely that,

\displaystyle R_{1}^{2}={2k_{1}\over\ell_{1}}\hskip 57.81621ptR_{2}^{2}={2k_{2}\over\ell_{2}}\hskip 36.135ptk_{i},\ell_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(13.6.9)

The complex structure of the spacetime torus, which we will denote as
\rho
to avoid confusion with the complex structure
\tau
of the worldsheet torus, is given by
\rho=iR_{1}/R_{2}
up to
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations. Hence
\rho
satisfies a quadratic equation with integer coefficients,

\displaystyle\ell_{1}k_{2}\rho^{2}+\ell_{2}k_{1}=0

(13.6.10)

This is precisely the condition that the spacetime
T_{2}
admit complex multiplication. Though we have restricted ourselves to tori which are trivial products of circles here, one can argue by similar methods that for generic elliptic curves
{\mathbb{C}}/L
, rationality of the CFT is tantamount to the presence of complex multiplication on
{\mathbb{C}}/L
.

and was generalized to the Heterotic string in

\mathfrak{c}=1
unitary conformal field theories in

[

, while toroidal compactification of the superstring, T-duality, and related topics are discussed in detail in the second volume of Polchinski’s book

as well as in the book by Kiritsis

. Further useful references on correlation functions for compact scalars, representations of current and Kac-Moody algebras and related topics may be found in
[

and
[

]
. For the contents of section
13.6
, we refer again to
[

, and to section

on complex multiplication of these lecture notes.

14

S-duality of Type IIB superstrings

In this section, we shall draw together a number of different strands of inquiry addressed earlier in these lecture notes. We shall study the interplay between superstring amplitudes, their low energy effective interactions, Type IIB supergravity, and the S-duality symmetry of Type IIB superstring theory. We begin with a brief review of Type IIB supergravity which, in particular, provides the massless sector of Type IIB superstring theory. We then discuss how the
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
symmetry of Type IIB supergravity is reduced to the
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
symmetry of Type IIB superstring theory via an anomaly. We conclude with a discussion of how the low energy effective interactions induced by string theory on supergravity may be organized in terms of modular functions and forms under this
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
symmetry, and match the predictions provided by perturbative calculations of section
12
.

14.1

Type IIB supergravity

In section
12
we discussed the expansion of bosonic and superstring amplitudes in powers of the string coupling constant
g_{s}
. While this expansion produces only an asymptotic series in
g_{s}
, it has the advantage of being valid for all energy ranges. A different, and complementary, expansion of string theory is in powers of
\alpha^{\prime}
, also referred to as the low energy expansion. This is the expansion that was used for the string amplitudes in subsection
12.7
. To leading order in this approximation, one omits the effects of all the massive string states, whose mass-squares are set by the string scale
1/\alpha^{\prime}
. The remaining massless states precisely make up the contents of the corresponding supergravity.

To each 10-dimensional perturbative string theory, namely Type I, Type IIA, Type IIB, and Heterotic
E_{8}\times E_{8}
and
{\rm Spin}(32)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
there is a corresponding supergravity. The Type II theories have the maximal number of 32 supersymmetries, while the other three have 16 supersymmetries. We shall focus here on Type IIB as its corresponding Type IIB superstring theory has
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
duality symmetry, which is the focus of these lecture notes.

The contents of Type IIB supergravity consist of the following fields,

\displaystyle G\,

the metric, whose excitations are the graviton

\displaystyle C_{2}

the complex 2-form potential

\displaystyle\tau\,

the complex axion/dilaton field

\displaystyle C_{4}

the real 4-form potential, whose field strength is self-dual

\displaystyle\psi\,

chirality
+
Weyl spinor gravitino

\displaystyle\lambda\,

chirality
-
Weyl spinor dilatino

(14.1.1)

The fields
G,C_{2},\tau
, and
C_{4}
are bosonic fields, while
\psi,\lambda
are fermions. Here we shall concentrate on the properties and field equations of the bosonic fields. The field strength of
C_{2}
is
F_{3}=dC_{2}
, while the field strengths of
\tau
and
C_{4}
are given as follows,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=f^{2}\,dB

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=(1+i\tau)/(1-i\tau)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=f^{2}\,{\rm Im\,}(Bd\bar{B})

\displaystyle f^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-|B|^{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle F_{5}

\displaystyle=dC_{4}+\tfrac{i}{16}\big(C_{2}\wedge\bar{F}_{3}-\bar{C}_{2}\wedge F_{3}\big)

\displaystyle\star F_{5}

\displaystyle=F_{5}

(14.1.2)

The axion-dilaton field
\tau
is valued in the hyperbolic upper half-plane
\tau_{2}={\rm Im\,}\tau>0
. The field equation for
F_{5}
coincides with its Bianchi identity
dF_{5}=\tfrac{i}{8}F_{3}\wedge\bar{F}_{3}
in view of its self-duality relation. In terms of the components of the fields
P,Q,F_{5}
, and
K=f(F_{3}-B\bar{F}_{3})
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=P_{\mu}\,dx^{\mu}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3!}K_{\mu\nu\rho}\,dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}\wedge dx^{\rho}

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=Q_{\mu}\,dx^{\mu}

\displaystyle F_{5}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{5!}F_{5\mu\nu\rho\sigma\tau}\,dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}\wedge dx^{\rho}\wedge dx^{\sigma}\wedge dx^{\tau}

(14.1.3)

the remaining field equations are conveniently expressed as follows,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}P_{\mu}-2iQ^{\mu}P_{\mu}+\tfrac{1}{24}K_{\mu\nu\rho}K^{\mu\nu\rho}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\rho}K_{\mu\nu\rho}-iQ^{\rho}K_{\mu\nu\rho}-P^{\rho}\bar{K}_{\mu\nu\rho}+\tfrac{2i}{3}F_{5\mu\nu\rho\sigma\tau}K^{\rho\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-P_{\mu}\bar{P}_{\nu}-\bar{P}_{\mu}P_{\nu}-\tfrac{1}{6}(F_{5}^{2})_{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm Re\,}(K_{\mu}{}^{\rho\sigma}\bar{K}_{\nu\rho\sigma})+\tfrac{1}{48}G_{\mu\nu}K^{\rho\tau\sigma}\bar{K}_{\rho\tau\sigma}

(14.1.4)

where
R_{\mu\nu}
is the Ricci tensor for the metric
G_{\mu\nu}
. The axion-dilaton field
{\tau}
may be related to the axion field
\chi
and the dilaton field
\Phi
,

\displaystyle{\tau}=\chi+ie^{-\Phi}

(14.1.5)

In view of the self-duality condition on
F_{5}
, there does not exist a Lorentz-covariant action from which the field equations of Type IIB supergravity may be deduced using the standard variational principle. Instead one may define the following action,
26
26

26

Our notation for the contraction of two rank
n
anti-symmetric tensors is
F_{n}\cdot F^{\prime}_{n}=\tfrac{1}{n!}F_{n\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n}}F_{n}^{\prime\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n}}
.

\displaystyle S_{{\rm IIB}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2\kappa_{10}^{2}}\int d\mu_{G}\left(R-{\partial_{\mu}{\tau}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\tau}\over 2{\tau}_{2}^{2}}-{(F_{3}^{1}-{\tau}F_{3}^{2})\cdot(F_{3}^{1}-\bar{\tau}F_{3}^{2})\over 2\,{\tau}_{2}}-{1\over 4}F_{5}\cdot F_{5}\right)

(14.1.6)

\displaystyle-{2\over\kappa_{10}^{2}}\int C_{4}\wedge F_{3}^{1}\wedge F_{3}^{2}

Here
d\mu_{G}
is the invariant volume form for the metric
G
;
\kappa_{10}^{2}
is related to the 10-dimensional Newton constant; and we have
F_{3}=F_{3}^{1}+iF_{3}^{2}
and
{\tau}={\tau}_{1}+i{\tau}_{2}
for real field components
F_{3}^{1},F_{3}^{2},{\tau}_{1},{\tau}_{2}
. Importantly, the action
S_{{\rm IIB}}
should be considered for a field
F_{5}
that is unconstrained by the self-duality condition. The field equations for Type IIB supergravity may be obtained from this action by varying the unconstrained field
F_{5}
, along with all the other fields, and only subsequently imposing the self-duality condition
\star F_{5}=F_{5}
.

14.2

From
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
to
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

The Type IIB supergravity field equations are invariant under the group
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, under which the metric
G
and the anti-symmetric fields
C_{4}
and
F_{5}
are invariant, and the fields
P,Q,K,\tau
and
F_{3}=F_{3}^{1}+iF_{3}^{2}
transform as follows,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\to P^{\prime}=e^{2i\theta}P

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\to\tau^{\prime}=(a\tau+b)/(c\tau+d)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\to Q^{\prime}=Q+d\theta

\displaystyle F_{3}^{1}

\displaystyle\to(F_{3}^{1})^{\prime}=aF_{3}^{1}+bF_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\to K^{\prime}=e^{i\theta}K

\displaystyle F_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle\to(F_{3}^{2})^{\prime}=cF_{3}^{1}+dF_{3}^{2}

(14.2.1)

The variable
\theta
is given by,

\displaystyle e^{i\theta}=\left({(\tau+i)(a\bar{\tau}-ic\bar{\tau}+b-id)\over(\bar{\tau}-i)\big(a\tau+ic\tau+b+id\big)}\right)^{1\over 2}

(14.2.2)

The axion-dilaton takes values in the coset
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/U(1)
and the phase
e^{i\theta}
performs a field-dependent
U(1)
rotation. The gravitino field
\psi
and the dilatino field
\lambda
transform with
U(1)
phase factors under
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, given by
\psi\to\psi^{\prime}=e^{i\theta/2}\psi
and
\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime}=e^{3i\theta/2}\lambda
.

The
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
symmetry discussed above is a symmetry of the classical supergravity field equations, as well as of the action
S_{{\rm IIB}}
and the self-duality condition
\star F_{5}=F_{5}
. A classical symmetry cannot always be promoted to a symmetry of the corresponding quantized field theory, in which case the symmetry is said to suffer an anomaly. Phase rotations on chiral fermions often suffer anomalies, and this is also the case here with the field-dependent
U(1)
transformations acting on the gravitino and dilatino chiral fermions. A treatment of anomalies is beyond the scope of this paper, so we shall just state the result.

It turns out that the continuous
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
symmetry is broken by the
U(1)
-anomaly, but the quantum theory retains a discrete symmetry, under which the axion field is shifted by an integer
\chi\to\chi^{\prime}=\chi+b
with
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The discrete nature of the remaining symmetry is due to the fact that the axion field couples to the topological charge density of the
D_{-1}
instantons in Type IIB superstring theory, whose total charge is quantized. The shift in the axion field
\chi\to\chi^{\prime}=\chi+b
with
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
that survives quantization also shifts the complex axion-dilaton field
{\tau}\to{\tau}^{\prime}={\tau}+b
, and is supplemented by the transformation
{\tau}\to-1/{\tau}
. Together these transformations generate the discrete group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. This symmetry extends to a symmetry of the entire Type IIB superstring theory.

This phenomenon is actually familiar from an extension of the Standard Model in which an axion field is included. Continuous shifts in the axion field again suffer an anomaly but discrete shifts survive. In this case the discrete nature of the shift is due to the coupling of the axion field to the topological charge density of Yang-Mills instantons whose topological charge is quantized in terms of the second Chern class of the gauge group.

14.3

Low energy effective interactions

Supergravity, as a classical theory, is valid for all values of the string coupling. Thus, it permits investigations into certain strong coupling phenomena such as solitonic states, NS branes and D-branes. Supergravity has been an invaluable tool in the search for semi-realistic compactifications of superstring theory, such as on tori, oribifolds, and Calabi-Yau spaces.

However, supergravity alone is not a consistent quantum theory. In 10 space-time dimensions, it exhibits UV divergences starting at one loop. The UV convergence situation is somewhat improved by lowering the dimension of space-time. In four space-time dimensions the four-graviton amplitude is UV convergent up to five loops, but it is likely that non-renormalizable UV divergences start occurring at seven loops, rendering the quantum theory inconsistent, or more accurately, incomplete. Its UV completion is precisely superstring theory. From the vantage point of superstring theory, supergravity should be thought of as an effective low energy field theory that captures the dynamics of the massless states of Type IIB string theory, to leading order in the
\alpha^{\prime}
expansion.

The validity of an effective field theory may be extended beyond its leading order contributions. Viewed as an effective field theory of Type IIB superstring theory, the corresponding supergravity may be supplemented with contributions of higher order in
\alpha^{\prime}
. Such contributions are due to the effects of massive string states whose mass, we recall, is of order
1/\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
and are referred to as effective interactions In the approximation where the momenta used to probe string amplitudes are small compared to
1/\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
, the effective interactions are local, as is illustrated in Figure
15
for the exchange of a massive string state. Although these string induced effective interactions are highly suppressed they provide systematic corrections to supergravity in an expansion in powers of
\alpha^{\prime}
.

\bullet

Figure 15
:
The exchange of a massive string state, indicated by the thick line in the left panel, induces a local effective interaction indicated by a thick dot in the right panel.

The space-time we observe has dimension four and is approximately flat when its curvature is measured in units of the Planck scale. Therefore, if the space-time of superstring theory is to be 10 then the six extra dimensions must have radii that are smaller than the smallest length scales accessible to experiment today. Physically realistic superstring theories are often based on space-times of the form
{\mathbb{R}}^{10-d}\times M_{d}
where
M_{d}
is a compact manifold or orbifold and the physically observed dimension of space time corresponds to
d=6
. For the case where
M_{d}
is a flat torus, superstring perturbation theory continues to be well-understood. When
M_{d}
is an orbifold of a torus, the space
M_{d}
is flat away from isolated point-like singularities, and string theory on toroidal orbifold spaces still lends itself to reasonably explicit solutions. However, when
M_{d}
is an arbitrary curved manifold, the predictions of superstring theory are much more difficult to obtain. This is the case even when space-time is a curved maximally symmetric space such as
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
, for which the quantization of the superstring is still largely an unresolved problem. An important exception is when the curvature of
M_{d}
is uniformly small compared to the Planck scale, a case that is referred to as the large radius expansion, and that we shall discuss next.

Restricting, for example, to purely gravitational effects, the supergravity Lagrangian reduces to the Einstein-Hilbert term given by the Ricci scalar for the space-time metric. In Type II superstring theory, for example, the lowest order
\alpha^{\prime}
correction to the Einstein-Hilbert action is given by a term which we symbolically represent by
{\cal R}^{4}
where
{\cal R}
stands for the Riemann tensor, and the contribution to the action is obtained via a special contraction of the four factors, consistent with space-time supersymmetry, as will be explained below. This effective interaction arises at order
(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}
. Higher order effective interactions involve more derivatives and higher powers of
\alpha^{\prime}
and are symbolically represented by
D^{2k}{\cal R}^{4}
where
D
represents a covariant derivative, again suitably contracted.

14.4

SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
duality in Type IIB superstring theory

Type IIB superstring theory exhibits
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
duality symmetry which leaves the space-time metric
G_{E}
in Einstein frame and the self-dual five form invariant, and transforms the 3-form flux field strengths linearly into one another,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}F_{3}^{1}\cr F_{3}^{2}\end{matrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}F_{3}^{1}\cr F_{3}^{2}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(14.4.1)

As for the axion-dilaton field obtained by combining the dilaton field
\Phi
and the axion field
\chi
into a complex scalar field
{\tau}=\chi+ie^{-\Phi}
, transformations under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
occur as follows,

\displaystyle{\tau}\to{a{\tau}+b\over c{\tau}+d}

(14.4.2)

The transformation properties of the fermionic fields are more complicated, and will not be reproduced here. In fact, when one accounts for the fermionic fields the true duality symmetry of Type IIB is not
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, but rather an extension of it known as the meta-linear group
ML^{+}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
; see
[
255
]
for details. This subtlety will not affect the purely bosonic analysis below.

Being a symmetry of Type IIB string theory,
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
must be a symmetry of the low energy effective action of the theory. The effective interactions accessible from the four-graviton amplitude are of the form
D^{2k}{\cal R}^{4}
, as explained above, but they have coefficients that depend upon the vacuum expectation value of the dilaton
g_{s}=e^{\Phi}
and the axion
\chi
. Expressing the effective action in terms of the Einstein frame metric
G_{E}
, with associated Riemann tensor
{\cal R}_{E}
, and the vacuum expectation value
\tau
, we find to order
(\alpha^{\prime})^{7}
,

\displaystyle\int d\mu_{G}\Big({\cal E}_{0}({\tau}){\cal R}_{E}^{4}+{\cal E}_{4}({\tau})D^{4}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}+{\cal E}_{6}({\tau})D^{6}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}+{\cal E}_{8}({\tau})D^{8}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}+{\cal O}((\alpha^{\prime})^{8})\Big)

(14.4.3)

where the coefficients
{\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})
are real-valued scalar functions of
{\tau}
. Derivatives of
\tau
may occur as well but will be systematically omitted here for simplicity. Since the action must be
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant and, in Einstein frame,
G_{E}
and
{\cal R}_{E}
are invariant as well, we see that each coefficient
{\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})
must be invariant,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{2k}\left({a{\tau}+b\over c{\tau}+d}\right)={\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(14.4.4)

a property which makes them into non-holomorphic modular functions.

String perturbation theory calculations are carried out in the string frame metric which we shall denote by
G_{\mu\nu}
and which is related to the Einstein frame metric by
G_{\mu\nu}=e^{\Phi/2}G_{E\mu\nu}
. Converting the expression for the effective action of (
14.4.3
) to string frame may be done by using the following relations (for constant values of
\tau
),

\displaystyle\sqrt{G_{E}}\,{\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})\,D_{E}^{2k}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}=e^{(k-1)\Phi/2}\,\sqrt{G}\,{\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})\,D^{2k}{\cal R}^{4}

(14.4.5)

where
D
and
{\cal R}
now stand respectively for the covariant derivative and Riemann tensor in the string frame. Now let us carry out the following exercise: express combinations of the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series with effective interactions
D_{E}^{2k}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}
in the Einstein frame such that the leading perturbative contribution in string frame is tree-level,

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\pi^{3/2}\sqrt{G_{E}}\,E_{{3\over 2}}({\tau}){\cal R}_{E}^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\Phi}\zeta(3){\cal R}^{4}+{\pi^{2}\over 3}{\cal R}^{4}+\cdots

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\pi^{5/2}\sqrt{G_{E}}\,E_{{5\over 2}}({\tau})D_{E}^{4}{\cal R}_{E}^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\Phi}\zeta(5)D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}+{2\pi^{4}\over 135}e^{2\Phi}D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}+\cdots

(14.4.6)

Comparing the terms in the above table which have the
\Phi
-dependence of tree-level perturbative contributions with the known result of (
12.10.17
) at genus one and (
12.11.3
) and (
12.11.7
) at genus two, we see that their dependence on odd zeta-values is precisely reproduced. This observation would suggest that the coefficients
{\cal E}_{2k}({\tau})
in (
14.4.3
) may be given by the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series.

14.5

Eisenstein series from supersymmetry and S-duality

We will now show how supersymmetry and S-duality can be used to prove the identifications between
{\cal E}_{2k}(\tau)
and the appropriate Eisenstein series. We will begin with the case of
{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)
, namely the coefficient of
{\cal R}^{4}
. Because the metric
G
is in the same supermultiplet as all of the other supergravity fields reviewed in section
14.1
, supersymmetry requires that the
{\cal R}^{4}
term in the low energy effective action be accompanied by a number of analogous terms related by supersymmetry. To make this more concrete, we first introduce some notation. As we have discussed above, the complex 2-form potential
C_{2}
, and consequently the corresponding field strength
F_{3}
, transforms as a doublet under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. It is useful to package these field strengths into an
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
singlet via,

\displaystyle M={e^{i\phi}\over\sqrt{-2i\tau_{2}}}(F_{3}^{1}-\tau F_{3}^{2})

(14.5.1)

where the superscript on
F_{3}
is a doublet index. We further define the “super-covariantized” combination,

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\mu\nu\rho}=M_{\mu\nu\rho}-3\overline{\psi}_{[\mu}\gamma_{\nu\rho]}\lambda-6i\overline{\psi}^{*}_{[\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\psi_{\rho]}

(14.5.2)

obtained by dressing
M
with appropriate combinations of the gravitino and dilatino fields
\psi
and
\lambda
. The combination
\widehat{M}
has the property that under supersymmetry transformations it does not contain derivatives of the transformation parameter.

In terms of this supercovariant field strength, the supersymmetric completion to the
{\cal R}^{4}
term can be written as
[
256
]
,

\displaystyle S^{(3)}=\int d\mu_{G}\left({\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}\lambda^{16}+{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}\widehat{M}\lambda^{14}+\dots+{\cal E}_{0}^{(8,-8)}\widehat{M}^{8}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\left.+\dots+{\cal E}_{0}^{(0,0)}{\cal R}^{4}+\dots+{\cal E}_{0}^{(-12,12)}(\lambda^{*})^{16}\right)

(14.5.3)

where
\lambda^{16}
and
\widehat{M}\lambda^{14}
(which will be the main terms of interest to us below) are short for the following contractions,

\displaystyle\lambda^{16}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 16!}\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{16}}\lambda^{a_{1}}\dots\lambda^{a_{16}}

\displaystyle\widehat{M}\lambda^{14}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 14!}\widehat{M}_{\mu\nu\rho}(\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\gamma^{0})_{a_{15}a_{16}}\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{16}}\lambda^{a_{1}}\dots\lambda^{a_{14}}

(14.5.4)

The
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
symmetry of Type IIB demands that the coefficient functions
{\cal E}_{0}^{(w,-w)}(\tau)
be modular forms of holomorphic weight
w
and anti-holomorphic weight
-w
. The coefficient function
{\cal E}_{0}^{(0,0)}(\tau)={\cal E}_{0}(\tau)
is the original modular invariant coefficient function of
{\cal R}^{4}
. The coefficient functions are furthermore required by supersymmetry to satisfy

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{0}^{(w,-w)}(\tau)=D_{w-1}\dots D_{0}{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)

(14.5.5)

with
D_{w}=i\tau_{2}\left({\partial\over\partial\tau}-i{w\over 2\tau_{2}}\right)
the covariant derivative mapping weight
(w,w^{\prime})
modular forms to weight
(w+1,w^{\prime}-1)
modular forms. Because of these relations, constraints on
{\cal E}_{0}^{(w,-w)}
for any
w
can imply non-trivial constraints on
{\cal E}_{0}
. We will now see how to use supersymmetry to obtain differential constraints on
{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}
and
{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}
in particular, which will suffice to prove that
{\cal E}_{0}
is, up to normalization, equivalent to
E_{3\over 2}(\tau)
.

Let us begin by schematically writing the low-energy effective action as

\displaystyle S={1\over(\alpha^{\prime})^{4}}\left[S^{(0)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}S^{(3)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{4}S^{(4)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{5}S^{(5)}+\dots\right]

(14.5.6)

with
S^{(3)}
in particular given in (
14.5
) above. Likewise, a generic SUSY transformation
\delta
can be expanded in powers of
\alpha^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\delta=\delta^{(0)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}\delta^{(3)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{4}\delta^{(4)}+(\alpha^{\prime})^{5}\delta^{(5)}+\dots

(14.5.7)

Requiring that the action has supersymmetry implies that we must have

\displaystyle\delta^{(0)}S^{(0)}=\delta^{(0)}S^{(3)}+\delta^{(3)}S^{(0)}=\delta^{(0)}S^{(5)}+\delta^{(5)}S^{(0)}=\dots=0

(14.5.8)

on the equations of motion.

We now consider the first two terms in the action,

\displaystyle{\cal L}^{(3)}={\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}\lambda^{16}+{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}\widehat{M}\lambda^{14}

(14.5.9)

A crucial feature of these two terms is that they are related by a subset of the SUSY transformations that do not mix with any of the other terms at this order, though we will have to keep track of variations of the lowest order action
S^{(0)}
by
\delta^{(3)}
. It is a straightforward exercise to find the lowest-order
\delta^{(0)}
supersymmetry variation of
{\cal L}^{(3)}
. Denoting the supersymmetry parameter by
\epsilon
and keeping only terms proportional to
\lambda^{16}\psi_{\mu}^{*}\epsilon
gives

\displaystyle\delta^{(0)}{\cal L}^{(3)}|_{\lambda^{16}\psi_{\mu}^{*}\epsilon}=-8i\left({\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)+108D_{11}{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}(\tau)\right)

(14.5.10)

On the other hand, the
\delta^{(3)}
variation of
S^{(0)}
cannot produce any such term. Hence we require
\delta^{(0)}{\cal L}^{(3)}|_{\lambda^{16}\psi_{\mu}^{*}\epsilon}=0
alone, which demands

\displaystyle D_{11}{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}(\tau)=-{1\over 108}{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)

(14.5.11)

Further constraints can be obtained by considering the term in the supersymmetry variation proportional to
\lambda^{16}\lambda^{*}\epsilon^{*}
. In this case the computation is slightly more involved since now there is a contribution from the
\delta^{(3)}
variation of
S^{(0)}
. The analysis will not be reproduced here, but the result is the following constraint

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{-12}\,{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)+3240\,{\cal E}_{0}^{(11,-11)}(\tau)-90g=0

(14.5.12)

where
g(\tau,\overline{\tau})
is an unknown function. A final constraint may be obtained by demanding closure of the SUSY algebra on
\lambda^{*}
, giving

\displaystyle 32D_{11}g={\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)

(14.5.13)

We have now obtained three constraints (
14.5.11
), (
14.5.12
), and (
14.5.13
). Combining them, we may derive a Laplace eigenvalue equation for
{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}
,

\displaystyle\Delta\,{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)=\left(-132+{3\over 4}\right){\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)

(14.5.14)

where we have noted that, when acting on weight-
(12,-12)
modular functions, the Laplacian is given by
\Delta=4D_{11}\overline{D}_{-12}
.

It remains only to translate this differential constraint on
{\cal E}_{0}^{(12,-12)}(\tau)
to a differential constraint on
{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)
, which can be done using the relation (
14.5.5
) between the two. This gives the following Laplace eigenvalue equation for
{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)={3\over 4}{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)

(14.5.15)

Together with the asymptotic expansion obtained from string perturbation theory, this equation uniquely fixes
{\cal E}_{0}(\tau)
to be the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{3\over 2}(\tau)
. Indeed, we saw already in (
4.2.5
) that the Eisenstein series satisfy precisely such eigenvalue equations.

We next briefly outline the analogous calculation for the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
term. As before, the first step is to identify a subset of the terms appearing in
S^{(5)}
which mix with each other, but not with other terms, under some subset of the supersymmetry transformations. In the current case, a judicious choice is the following,

\displaystyle{\cal L}^{(5)}=\lambda^{16}\widehat{M}^{4}{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}+\lambda^{15}\gamma^{\mu}\psi_{\mu}^{*}\widehat{M}^{4}{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}+\lambda^{16}\widehat{M}^{2}\widehat{M}_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\rho_{3}}\widehat{M}^{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\rho_{3}}\widetilde{{\cal E}}_{4}^{(13,-13)}

(14.5.16)

As before, modular invariance demands that the coefficient functions
{\cal E}_{4}^{(w,-w)}
be modular forms of weight-
(w,-w)
, with
{\cal E}_{4}^{(0,0)}={\cal E}_{4}
the coefficient of
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
itself. Note that there are two coefficient functions of weight-
(13,-13)
above, which we have denoted by
{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}
and
\widetilde{{\cal E}}_{4}^{(13,-13)}
. We now proceed by again checking invariance under supersymmetry. Considering the term proportional to
\lambda^{16}\psi_{\mu}^{*}\epsilon
, we find only the contribution from
\delta^{(0)}{\cal L}^{(5)}
, and demanding that this vanishes gives the constraint

\displaystyle 2D_{13}\,{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}-11\,{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}=0

(14.5.17)

Considering instead the term proportional to
\lambda^{16}\lambda^{*}\epsilon^{*}
, we obtain both a contribution from the
\delta^{(0)}
variation of
{\cal L}^{(5)}
as well as the
\delta^{(5)}
variation of
S^{(0)}
. Demanding the vanishing of the combination gives the constraint

\displaystyle 2\overline{D}_{-14}\,{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}+15\,{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}-{9i\over 16}\,\widetilde{{\cal E}}_{4}^{(13,-13)}-1080i\,g_{1}-{3\over 4}i\,g_{2}=0

(14.5.18)

with
g_{1}(\tau,\overline{\tau})
and
g_{2}(\tau,\overline{\tau})
undetermined functions. Closure of the supersymmetry algebra on
\lambda^{*}
further gives rise to the following three equations,

\displaystyle-192i\,D_{13}g_{1}={\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}\hskip 21.68121pt-108\,g_{1}=\widetilde{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}\hskip 21.68121pti\,g_{2}+191ig_{1}={1\over 2}{\cal E}_{4}^{(13,-13)}

(14.5.19)

These constraints can then be combined to obtain a Laplace eigenvalue equation for
{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}
, namely

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}=\left(-182+{15\over 4}\right)\,{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}

(14.5.20)

Recalling that
{\cal E}_{4}^{(14,-14)}=D_{13}\dots D_{0}{\cal E}_{4}
, we conclude that
{\cal E}_{4}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal E}_{4}(\tau)={15\over 4}\,{\cal E}_{4}(\tau)

(14.5.21)

This eigenvalue equation, together with the asymptotics at the cusp, fix
{\cal E}_{4}
to be equal to
E_{5/2}(\tau)
up to an overall constant factor.

Finally, for the coefficient of the
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
term, analogous steps can again be carried out, though one now obtains an inhomogeneous Laplace eigenvalue equation,

\displaystyle(\Delta-12){\cal E}_{6}(\tau)=-6\pi^{3}E_{3/2}(\tau)^{2}

(14.5.22)

the solution to which is no longer an Eisenstein series. No closed form solution is known to this differential equation, but it is possible to get a closed form expression for the Laurent polynomial
{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}
of
{\cal E}_{6}
, namely the portions of
{\cal E}_{6}
which are power-law in
\tau_{2}
and independent of
\tau_{1}
. Fortunately, the Laurent polynomial pieces are the most interesting for the present purposes, since they are the ones which can be compared to calculations in string perturbation theory.

To obtain the Laurent polynomial
{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}(\tau)
we begin by inserting the Fourier series of
E_{3/2}(\tau)
, given in (
4.2.16
), into (
14.5.22
) and keeping only the Laurent polynomial pieces,

\displaystyle(\Delta-12){\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-6\left(\left(2\zeta(3)\tau_{2}^{3/2}+{2\over 3}\pi^{2}\tau_{2}^{-1/2}\right)^{2}\right.

(14.5.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\left.+64\pi^{2}\tau_{2}\sum_{N\neq 0}|N|^{-2}\sigma_{2}(|N|)^{2}K_{1}(2\pi\tau_{2}|N|)^{2}\right)

Because the Laplacian is given by
\Delta=\tau_{2}^{2}(\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2})
, and hence the derivatives
\partial_{\tau_{2}}^{2}
are dressed with factors of
\tau_{2}^{2}
, the function
{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}
must be a polynomial in
\tau_{2}
with powers being those appearing on the right side, namely
\tau_{2}^{3}
,
\tau_{2}
, and
\tau_{2}^{-1}
, together with the powers solving the homogeneous equation
(\Delta-12)f=0
, namely
\tau_{2}^{-3}
and
\tau_{2}^{4}
. The term
\tau_{2}^{4}
can be ruled out on physical grounds, since there should be no term more singular than the tree-level
\tau_{2}^{3}
term. On the other hand, the
\tau_{2}^{-3}
contribution corresponds to a three-loop contribution which can be (and indeed is) non-zero.

The general Ansatz for
{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}(\tau)
is then,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}(\tau)=a_{1}\tau_{2}^{3}+a_{2}\tau_{2}+a_{3}\tau_{2}^{-1}+a_{4}\tau_{2}^{-3}

(14.5.24)

By plugging into (
14.5.22
) we may uniquely fix
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
, giving,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{6}^{(0)}(\tau)=4\zeta(3)^{2}\tau_{2}^{3}+{4\over 3}\pi^{2}\zeta(3)\tau_{2}+{4\pi^{4}\over 15}\tau_{2}^{-1}+a_{4}\tau_{2}^{-3}

(14.5.25)

These match with the expectations from string perturbation given in (
12.7.4
), (
12.10.17
), and (
12.11.7
) at tree-level, one-loop, and two-loops, respectively. As for the three-loop coefficient
a_{4}
, this cannot be fixed by simply inserting the Ansatz into (
14.5.22
), since this term already solves the homogenous equation. Instead, this coefficient was fixed in
[
257
]
by multiplying both sides of the Laplace equation by
E_{4}(\tau)
and integrating over the fundamental domain, using the results presented in Lemma
12.1
. The final result is

\displaystyle a_{4}={8\pi^{6}\over 8505}

(14.5.26)

We note in closing that beyond
D^{6}{\cal R}_{4}
, the terms in the low energy effective action are no longer protected by supersymmetry, and thus we do not expect to be able to extend the above analysis to study them.

14.5.1

Non-renormalization Theorems

Perhaps the most striking implications of the modular structure of the
{\cal R}^{4}
,
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
, and
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
effective interactions is that they make predictions for the amplitudes at all orders of perturbation theory, i.e. all genera. From the Fourier series expansion of the functions
E_{3\over 2}({\tau})
we see that, aside from the leading perturbative contribution, there is only one other perturbative contribution, given by the second term, and behaving as
{\tau}_{2}^{-{1\over 2}}
. In the string frame, this gives a contribution of order
{\tau}_{2}^{0}
which corresponds to genus one. This means that from superstring perturbation theory, the
{\cal R}^{4}
effective interaction receives contributions from genus zero and genus one only. There are no contributions to genus 2 and higher. Similarly, for the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
effective interaction, there is a genus zero contribution and the only other contribution behaves as
{\tau}_{2}^{-{3\over 2}}
in the Einstein frame, or
{\tau}_{2}^{-2}
in the string frame. Hence there is no genus one correction to the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
effective interaction but there is a non-zero genus two contribution. There are no higher contributions.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

The existence of a web of dualities between different supergravities and string theories was proposed in
[

]
, and led to the discovery of M-theory in
[

]
. The action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on branes in Type IIB was developed in
[

, while evidence of the duality between Type I and Heterotic string theory was given in
[

. Useful lecture notes may be found in
[

, and an early account of the role played by automorphic functions in dualities is in

, and by Becker, Becker and Schwarz
[

provide excellent and comprehensive accounts of string dualities.

The use of S-duality to constrain the low-energy effective action goes back to
[
265
]
, where the coefficient of the
{\cal R}^{4}
term was related to the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series
E_{3/2}(\tau)
using results from tree-level string theory and D-instantons. A derivation using supersymmetry, in the way reviewed above, was provided in
[

]
and extended in
[
266
]
. That the same techniques could also be applied to the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
term was proposed in
[
256
]
, and used to provide a full derivation in
[
267
]
. As we have mentioned in the text, there is no closed form expression for the
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
term, though it is known to satisfy a Laplace eigenvalue equation; this property and others were discussed in
[

,

,
257
,
270
,

. An implicit expression for the
D^{6}{\cal R}^{4}
term as a two-loop Schwinger integral can be found in
[

.

As mentioned in section
14.5.1
, the results quoted in the previous paragraph imply a number of non-renormalization theorems, some of which were anticipated from superstring perturbation theory in
[

,

. For the
{\cal R}^{4}
term, the S-duality and supersymmetry analysis predict the absence of perturbative corrections beyond one-loop, and indeed the vanishing at genus two was proven in
[
228
]
. Conversely, for the
D^{4}{\cal R}^{4}
term there is expected to be no genus one correction, as was verified in
[
205
]
, but there is a non-zero genus-two contribution which was successfully computed and matched in
[

]
.

The systematic study of genus-one contributions to effective interactions of the form
D^{2\ell}{\cal R}^{4}
with

was initiated in
[

. From this grew the notion of modular graph functions
[

, introduced in section

One of the hallmarks of superstring theory is the infinite tower of BPS states, which includes black hole microstates. If the string background under consideration contains a torus, then the functions which count these microstates often enjoy nice modular or automorphic properties. A particularly well-studied setup is string theory compactified to 4d with
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry. This can be engineered by considering Type II superstrings on
K3\times T^{2}
or Heterotic strings on
T^{6}
. Either way, the theory is expected to have an
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\times SO(22,6,{\mathbb{Z}})
duality symmetry, with the corresponding counting functions expressible as Siegel modular forms
[
274
]
. The contribution of multi-centered black holes was studied in
[
275
]
, while the single-centered black holes were shown to have interesting connections with mock modular and Jacobi forms in
[

]
. Recent connections have been made with Hurwitz class numbers
[
276
]
and class groups
[
277
]
as well. More generally, the tools of Rademacher sums and Farey tail expansions have been applied to the study of microstate counting in
[
278
,
279
,
280
]
. A pedagogical introduction to the relation between blackhole microstate counting and automorphic forms can be found in
[
281
]
.

15

Dualities in
{\cal N}=2
super Yang-Mills theories

In this penultimate section, we shall discuss dualities in Yang-Mills theories with extended supersymmetry in four-dimensional Minkowski space-time. We briefly review supersymmetry multiplets of states and fields and the construction of supersymmetric Lagrangian theories with
{\cal N}=1,2,4
Poincaré supersymmetries. We then discuss the
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
Montonen-Olive duality properties of the maximally supersymmetric
{\cal N}=4
theory and the low energy effective Lagrangians for
{\cal N}=2
theories via the Seiberg-Witten solution. We shall close this section with a discussion of dualities of
{\cal N}=2
superconformal gauge theories, which possess interesting spaces of marginal gauge couplings. In some cases these spaces of couplings can be identified with the moduli spaces for Riemann surfaces of various genera.

15.1

Super Yang-Mills: states and fields

The quantum field theories we shall consider here are invariant under the Poincaré algebra, whose generators of translations
P_{\mu}
and Lorentz transformations
L_{\mu\nu}
satisfy the following structure relations,
27
27

27

Our conventions are as follows. The non-zero components of the Minkowski metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
are
-\eta_{00}=\eta_{11}=\eta_{22}=\eta_{33}=1
. The Clifford-Dirac algebra is
\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=-2\eta^{\mu\nu}I_{4}
, where
I_{d}
is the
d\times d
identity matrix. In a basis where the chirality matrix
\gamma_{5}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{2}&0\cr 0&-I_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is diagonal, we have
\gamma^{\mu}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\sigma^{\mu}\cr\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
where
\sigma^{0}=\bar{\sigma}^{0}=-I_{2}
and
\sigma^{i}=-\bar{\sigma}^{i}
are the standard Pauli matrices for
i=1,2,3
. Two-component spinor indices
\alpha,\dot{\alpha}
are raised and lowered with the help of the anti-symmetric symbols
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta},\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
and
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta},\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
normalized to
\epsilon^{12}=\epsilon^{\dot{1}\dot{2}}=-\epsilon_{12}=-\epsilon_{\dot{1}\dot{2}}=1
. We use the notations
\psi\chi=\psi^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}
,
\bar{\psi}\bar{\chi}=\bar{\psi}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}
, and
\chi\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\psi}=\chi^{\alpha}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\psi}^{\dot{\alpha}}
. For more details on spinor index notation, see for example
[
282
]
.

\displaystyle[L_{\mu\nu},L_{\rho\sigma}]

\displaystyle=\eta_{\nu\rho}L_{\mu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}L_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\rho}L_{\nu\sigma}-\eta_{\nu\sigma}L_{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle{[L_{\mu\nu},P_{\rho}]}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\nu\rho}P_{\mu}-\eta_{\mu\rho}P_{\nu}

\displaystyle{}[P_{\mu},P_{\nu}]

\displaystyle=0

(15.1.1)

In supersymmetric theories the Poincaré algebra is extended to a super Poincaré algebra by supplementing
P_{\mu}
and
L_{\mu\nu}
with
{\cal N}
supercharges
Q_{\alpha}^{I}
and their adjoints
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}I}=(Q^{I}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}
for spinor index
\alpha=1,2
and
I=1,\cdots,{\cal N}
. The supercharges commute with
P_{\mu}
, are Weyl spinors under the Lorentz generators
L_{\mu\nu}
, and obey the following anti-commutation relations,

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\beta}J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\,P_{\mu}\,\delta^{I}_{J}\hskip 43.36243ptI,J=1,\cdots,{\cal N}

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},Q_{\beta}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,Z^{IJ}

(15.1.2)

The generators
Z^{IJ}=-Z^{JI}
can arise only when
{\cal N}\geq 2
. They are central generators in the Poincaré superalgebra since they commute with one another and with all other generators. When
Z^{IJ}=0
, the supersymmetry algebra is invariant under a
U({\cal N})_{R}
automorphism group which, in Physics, is referred to as the R-symmetry group.

Applying a supercharge to a state changes the spin by
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
so that the values of the spin in the multiplets of the Poincaré superalgebra must increase with
{\cal N}
. When
{\cal N}>4
, the multiplets necessarily involve states of spin greater than 1, which cannot be accommodated in Yang-Mills theory. For
{\cal N}\leq 8
they can, however, be accommodated in supergravity, but this requires states of spin 2. Thus
{\cal N}=4
is the maximal number of Poincaré supersymmetries allowed for supersymmetric Yang-Mills theories in four dimensions.

15.1.1

States

The spectrum of states in a supersymmetric theory exhibits a characteristic pattern. All states have positive or zero energy. If the state of lowest energy in the spectrum—namely the ground state—has exactly zero energy then supersymmetry is said to be unbroken or manifest. In this case all positive energy states occur in boson-fermion pairs of equal mass, momentum, and internal quantum numbers. If the ground state has strictly positive energy, however, the ground state is not supersymmetric and supersymmetry is said to be spontaneously broken. Bosons and fermions of identical internal quantum numbers do not generally have the same mass.

In this section we shall consider only the case where supersymmetry is manifest. For the case of
{\cal N}=1
, this is the complete story. For
{\cal N}\geq 2
, there is an additional interplay between the mass of the state and its central charge.

For
{\cal N}=2
there is a single complex-valued central charge
Z=Z^{12}
, which we decompose into its absolute value and its phase,
Z=e^{i\varphi}|Z|
for
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We consider a state with mass
M\not=0
and use a Poincaré transformation to the rest frame of this state with momentum
P^{\mu}=(M,0,0,0)
. In terms of the combinations
{\cal Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}(Q^{1}_{\alpha}\mp e^{-i\varphi}\sigma^{0}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}Q^{2\dot{\beta}})
the anti-commutation relations of (
15.1
) are equivalent to the relations,

\displaystyle\big\{{\cal Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha},({\cal Q}^{\pm}_{\beta})^{\dagger}\big\}=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}(M\pm|Z|)

(15.1.3)

while all other anti-commutators vanish identically. Since the operators
\big\{{\cal Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha},({\cal Q}^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}\big\}
are self-adjoint and positive in a unitary theory, we obtain the following so-called BPS bound between the mass
M
and the central charge
Z
of any state,

\displaystyle M\geq|Z|

(15.1.4)

In the limit where the state becomes massless, its central charge must vanish. Of particular interest are the massive states for which the BPS bound is saturated, say by
M=|Z|
. In these states, which are referred to as BPS states, the supersymmetry algebra reduces to,

\displaystyle\big\{{\cal Q}^{+}_{\alpha},({\cal Q}^{+}_{\beta})^{\dagger}\big\}=2M\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\hskip 72.26999pt\big\{{\cal Q}^{-}_{\alpha},({\cal Q}^{-}_{\beta})^{\dagger}\big\}=0

(15.1.5)

while all other anti-commutators vanish. The operators
{\cal Q}^{-}_{\alpha}
and
({\cal Q}^{-}_{\alpha})^{\dagger}
both annihilate the BPS states which means that BPS states are invariant under half of the total number of Poincaré supersymmetries. More specifically they are referred to as
\tfrac{1}{2}
-BPS states, and they make up a supermultiplet that is half as long as the non-BPS supermultiplets.

For
{\cal N}=4
, the matrix of central charges
Z^{IJ}=-Z^{JI}
has two complex-valued eigenvalues
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
. The BPS bound is now
M\geq|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|
. When
|Z_{1}|\not=|Z_{2}|
one obtains
\tfrac{1}{2}
-BPS states by setting
M
equal to the maximum of
|Z_{1}|
and
|Z_{2}|
. But when
|Z_{1}|=|Z_{2}|
, a further shortening of the supersymmetry multiplet takes place to
\tfrac{1}{4}
-BPS states. It is a general result of CPT invariance in four space-time dimensions that supersymmetric Yang-Mills theories with
{\cal N}=3
automatically enjoy the full
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetry, so that the case
{\cal N}=3
need not be considered separately.

15.1.2

Fields

A super Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
G
and associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is constructed in terms of the customary fields of four-dimensional quantum field theory absent gravity, namely spin-1 gauge fields
A_{\mu}
in the adjoint representation of
G
, together with spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
left Weyl fermion fields
\psi_{\alpha},\lambda_{\alpha}
and spin-0 scalar fields
\phi,h
transforming under various representations of
G
. We recall that, in even dimensions, a Dirac spinor is the direct sum of two Weyl spinors of opposite chirality and that, in four dimensions, a Majorana spinor is equivalent to a Weyl spinor. The field multiplets for
{\cal N}=1,2,4
are as follows:

•

{\cal N}=1
Gauge multiplet
(A_{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha})
in the adjoint representation of
G
, where
A_{\mu}
is the gauge field and
\lambda_{\alpha}
is a left Weyl fermion referred to as the gaugino;

•

{\cal N}=1
Chiral multiplet
(\psi_{\alpha},\phi)
in a representation
{\cal R}
of
G
, where
\psi_{\alpha}
is a left Weyl fermion and
\phi
is a complex scalar field;

•

{\cal N}=2
Gauge multiplet
(A_{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha},\phi)
in the adjoint representation of
G
, where
A_{\mu}
and
\phi
are singlets under
SU(2)_{R}
while
(\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha})
is a doublet;

•

{\cal N}=2
Hypermultiplet
(h,\tilde{h},\psi_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha})
in a representation
{\cal R}
of
G
,
where
(h,\tilde{h})
is a doublet under
SU(2)_{R}
while
\psi_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}
are singlets;

•

{\cal N}=4
Gauge multiplet
(A_{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha}^{A},\phi^{I})
in the adjoint representation of
G
where
\lambda^{A}_{\alpha}
with
A=1,\cdots,4
are left Weyl fermions in the
{\bf 4}
of
SU(4)_{R}
and
\phi^{I}
with
I=1,\cdots,6
are complex scalars in the
{\bf 6}
of
SU(4)_{R}
while
A_{\mu}
is a singlet.

The fields of an
{\cal N}=1
theory may be collected in fully off-shell superfields with linear transformations under the action of the super Poincaré algebra, as will be made explicit in the next subsection. The construction of superfields for higher numbers of supersymmetries is, however, much more complicated and will not be addressed here. For this reason it will be useful to decompose the field content of the
{\cal N}=2
and
{\cal N}=4
theories in terms of
{\cal N}=1
superfields. In this spirit, the
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplet is the direct sum of an
{\cal N}=1
gauge multiplet and an
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplet, both in the adjoint representation of
G
. The
{\cal N}=2
hypermultiplet is a sum of two
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplets in complex conjugate representations of one another
{\cal R}\oplus\bar{\cal R}
. Similarly, the
{\cal N}=4
gauge multiplet is the direct sum of an
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplet and an
{\cal N}=2
hypermultiplet, both in the adjoint representation of
G
. Note that for
{\cal N}=4
theories the
U(1)_{R}
factor of the automorphism group
U({\cal N})_{R}
of the supersymmetry algebra is anomalous and fails to be a quantum symmetry. The charge assignments of the
U(1)_{R}
factor in the case of
{\cal N}=1
and
{\cal N}=2
will not be important in the sequel, and we shall not spell them out here.

Before moving on, we remark that when
{\cal R}
is a pseudoreal representation, and hence admits an antisymmetric invariant tensor
\epsilon_{IJ}
, the fields
h_{I}
and
\tilde{h}^{J}
in the
{\cal N}=2
hypermultiplet (with indices
I,J
in the representation
{\cal R}
shown explicitly) can be constrained to satisfy,

\displaystyle h_{I}=\epsilon_{IJ}\tilde{h}^{J}

(15.1.6)

This constraint is compatible with
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry, and halves the number of degrees of freedom in the multiplet. The resulting multiplet is referred to as a half-hypermultiplet. More generally, given a complex representation
{\cal R}
there is a standard pseudo-real structure on
{\cal R}\oplus\overline{{\cal R}}
, and hence we may define half-hypermultiplets in the representation
{\cal R}\oplus\overline{{\cal R}}
. These are precisely the same as full hypermultiplets in the complex representation
{\cal R}
.

15.2

Super Yang-Mills: Lagrangians

Two types of supersymmetric Lagrangians will be of interest to us: those that correspond to renormalizable quantum field theories, and those that collect the low energy effective interactions of some arbitrary Lagrangian or non-Lagrangian quantum field theory. In either case, we shall restrict attention to Lagrangians in which bosonic fields have a total of at most two derivatives while fermions have a total of at most one derivative acting on any field. Furthermore, the Lagrangians for theories with
{\cal N}=2
and
{\cal N}=4
supersymmetries will be expressed in terms of
{\cal N}=1
chiral and vector superfields, using the decomposition of the corresponding multiplets given in the penultimate paragraph of the preceding subsection. Throughout we shall assume that the gauge group
G
is compact with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and of the form of a semi-simple group times a certain number of
U(1)
factors.

To introduce
{\cal N}=1
superfields, we supplement the coordinates
x^{\mu}
of Minkowski space-time with Grassmann
spinor variables
\theta^{\alpha}
and
\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}
of odd grading. The superfield
\Phi
for the
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplet containing the fields
(\phi,\psi_{\alpha})
is given by,

\displaystyle\Phi=\phi+2\psi_{\alpha}\theta^{\alpha}+F\theta_{\alpha}\theta^{\alpha}

(15.2.1)

where
F
is a non-dynamical complex-valued scalar auxiliary field. The chiral superfield
\Phi
is generally matrix-valued with components
