\Phi^{i}
and transforms under an arbitrary representation
{\cal R}
of the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with
i=1,\cdots,\dim{\cal R}
. The superfield
V
for the
{\cal N}=1
gauge multiplet satisfies a reality condition
V^{\dagger}=V
and transforms in the adjoint representation of the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The general decomposition of the superfield
V
is more complicated than that of the chiral multiplet and we present it here in Wess-Zumino gauge only,

\displaystyle V=-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\overline{\theta}_{\mu}+i\theta\theta\bar{\theta}\bar{\lambda}-i\bar{\theta}\bar{\theta}\theta\lambda+{1\over 2}\theta\theta\bar{\theta}\bar{\theta}D

(15.2.2)

where
D
is a real-valued scalar auxiliary field. The vector superfield
V
is generally matrix-valued and may be decomposed
V=\sum_{a}T^{a}V^{a}
onto the generators
T^{a}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, with
a=1,\cdots,\dim\mathfrak{g}
, in the adjoint representation of the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

15.2.1

{\cal N}=1
Lagrangians

In terms of the
{\cal N}=1
gauge superfield
V
and chiral superfield
\Phi
, the most general Lagrangian density, under the assumptions spelled out earlier, is of the following form,

\displaystyle{\cal L}=\int d^{4}\theta\,K(e^{V}\Phi,\Phi^{\dagger})+\mathrm{Re}\int d^{2}\theta\Big(U(\Phi)+\tau_{ab}(\Phi)W^{a}W^{b}\Big)

(15.2.3)

in superspace notation. Here
K
is the real-valued Kähler potential,
U
is the superpotential,
and
\tau_{ab}
is the matrix of couplings and mixings of the field strengths
W_{\alpha}^{a}
of the gauge fields
V^{a}
. The functions
U(\Phi)
and
\tau_{ab}(\Phi)
are locally holomorphic in
\Phi
. The superfield
W_{\alpha}=\sum_{a}W_{\alpha}^{a}T^{a}
is obtained from the vector superfield
V
by taking,

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}=-{1\over 4}\bar{D}\bar{D}\left(e^{-V}D_{\alpha}e^{V}\right)\hskip 50.58878ptD_{\alpha}={\partial\over\partial\theta^{\alpha}}+i\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}

(15.2.4)

The field
W_{\alpha}
is a chiral superfield since
\bar{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\beta}=0
by construction.

15.2.2

Renormalizable
{\cal N}=2
Lagrangians

A general four-dimensional
{\cal N}=2
Lagrangian theory is specified by a gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
which determines the vector multiplets, as well as a representation
{\cal R}
of
\mathfrak{g}
for the hypermultiplets, or half-hypermultiplets if
{\cal R}
is pseudo-real.
28
28

28

This data is not quite complete: in general, additional global data such as the spectrum of line operators is necessary to fully specify the theory. We will discuss such additional data in the context of
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills in section
15.5
, but will otherwise largely ignore this subtlety. As long as one is concerned only with the local operator spectrum of the theory, no harm arises from this omission.

The
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplet consists of an
{\cal N}=1
gauge multiplet
V
and an
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplet
\Phi
in the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
, while the
{\cal N}=2
hypermultiplet consists of two
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplets
H,\tilde{H}
in the representations
{\cal R}
and
\bar{\cal R}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, respectively. The
{\cal N}=2
half-hypermultiplet consists of a single
{\cal N}=1
chiral multiplet
H
in a pseudo-real representation
{\cal R}
.

Let us consider first a renormalizable
{\cal N}=2
theory without hypermultiplets. We begin by arguing that the superpotential
U(\Phi)
must vanish. Indeed, for the theory to be renormalizable, one requires that
\tau_{ab}
be independent of
\Phi
and that the superpotential
U(\Phi)
be at most cubic in the chiral superfield
\Phi
.
Upon integrating over superspace, the final two terms of the Lagrangian (
15.2.3
) give,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\int d^{2}\theta\,U(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\Big\{F^{a}\partial_{a}U(\phi)+\psi^{a}\psi^{b}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}U(\phi)\Big\}

(15.2.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\int d^{2}\theta\,\tau_{ab}\,W^{a}W^{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Re}\left\{\tau_{ab}\left({i\over 2}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}F^{b\mu\nu}-{1\over 2}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}\tilde{F}^{b\mu\nu}+\overline{\lambda}^{a}\overline{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\lambda^{b}\right)\right\}

where
\partial_{a}=\partial/\partial\phi^{a}
. To obtain a Lagrangian with
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry, the combined contributions of the
{\cal N}=1
gauge and chiral multiplets must be invariant under the
SU(2)_{R}
symmetry rotating
\psi_{\alpha}
into
\lambda_{\alpha}
, which is possible only if
U(\Phi)
is linear in
\Phi
. Upon integrating out the auxiliary field
F
, a linear
U(\Phi)
would add a constant
|\partial U|^{2}
to the energy density and spontaneously break supersymmetry. Therefore, in a theory with supersymmetric vacua, the superpotential
U
is a constant that may set to zero without loss of generality.

What this means is that the only potential for the scalar fields
\phi^{a}
comes from the Kähler potential. To proceed, we shall assume for simplicity that the gauge group
G
is a simple Lie group, so that there is a single
{\cal N}=1
vector superfield
V
transforming under the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
. For a renormalizable theory, the Kähler potential must be canonical i.e.
K(e^{V}\Phi,\Phi^{\dagger})=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Phi^{\dagger}e^{V}\Phi\right)
. In that case the auxiliary field
D
may be integrated out using its field equations and the resulting scalar potential takes the form,

\displaystyle V_{{\rm gauge}}(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\mathrm{Tr}\left(i[\phi,\bar{\phi}]\right)^{2}

(15.2.6)

The structure of this potential will be of central importance in disentangling the vacuum structure of the
{\cal N}=2
theories.

Next, we include an
{\cal N}=2
hypermultiplet, which consists of
{\cal N}=1
chiral superfields
H=(h,\psi_{\alpha})
and
\tilde{H}=(\tilde{h},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha})
in representations
{\cal R}
and
\bar{\cal R}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, respectively. The representation
{\cal R}\oplus\bar{\cal R}
may be reducible into a direct sum of irreducible representations
{\cal R}_{I}
and
\bar{\cal R}_{I}
of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle{\cal R}=\bigoplus_{I}n_{I}\,{\cal R}_{I}\oplus\bar{\cal R}_{I}\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{I}n_{I}=N_{f}

(15.2.7)

where
n_{I}
is the number of hypermultiplets in representation
{\cal R}_{I}
, and
N_{f}
is the total number of hypermultiplets in all representations
{\cal R}_{I}
. We decompose the hypermultiplet accordingly into hypermultiplets
H_{I}
and
\tilde{H}_{I}
in the representations
{\cal R}_{I}
and
\bar{\cal R}_{I}
respectively. The scalar fields
h
and
\tilde{h}
in the hypermultiplets produce the following addition to the scalar potential,

\displaystyle V_{{\rm hyper}}(h,\tilde{h},\phi)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim{\cal R}}h_{i}(\phi_{a}T^{a})_{ij}\tilde{h}_{j}+\sum_{I}\sum_{s,t=1}^{n_{I}}M_{I,s,t}h_{I,s}\tilde{h}_{I,t}

(15.2.8)

Here
s,t
are flavor indices running from
1,\dots,n_{I}
in each irreducible representation
{\cal R}_{I}
, while
i,j
are the indices in the representation
{\cal R}
, and
\phi
is the scalar component of the superfield
\Phi
. The matrix
M_{I,s,t}
contains parameters that can be interpreted as masses and mixings of the hypermultiplets in a given representation
{\cal R}_{I}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Gauge invariance precludes mixings between hypermultiplets
{\cal R}_{I}
and
{\cal R}_{J}
with
I\not=J
. Combining this scalar potential with that in (
15.2.6
) allows for interesting structure for the moduli space of vacua.

15.3

Low-energy Lagrangian on the Coulomb branch

In a supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory, the energy is bounded from below by zero. In the sequel we shall assume that the gauge group of the UV theory is a compact semi-simple Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. In particular, the potential energy
V_{{\rm gauge}}
of (
15.2.6
) is non-negative since the Cartan-Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}
is negative definite. Any supersymmetric vacuum state has exactly zero energy, which requires vanishing fermion fields,
D_{\mu}\phi=F_{\mu\nu}=0
and, for the solutions to these equations in the gauge
A_{\mu}=0
, a constant field
\phi
satisfying,

\displaystyle[\phi,\bar{\phi}]=0

(15.3.1)

Decomposing
\phi=\phi_{1}+i\phi_{2}
into Hermitian matrices
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
gives the equivalent condition
[\phi_{1},\phi_{2}]=0
which guarantees that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
may be simultaneously diagonalized, and gives the following general solution to (
15.3.1
) for the vacuum expectation value of the field
\phi
,

\displaystyle\phi=\sum_{I=1}^{r}a_{I}\mathfrak{h}_{I}\hskip 72.26999ptr={\rm rank}(\mathfrak{g})

(15.3.2)

where
\mathfrak{h}_{I}
for
I=1,\cdots,r={\rm rank}(\mathfrak{g})
are the mutually commuting generators of the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. For generic values of
a_{I}\not=0
the vacuum is referred to as the Coulomb branch. The standard Higgs mechanism breaks the gauge symmetry
G
to the Cartan subgroup
U(1)^{r}
. Actually, the Cartan subgroup is invariant under the Weyl group
{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
group which is the residual symmetry remaining from the non-Abelian part of
G
. Thus, the precise symmetry breaking pattern is as follows,

\displaystyle G\longrightarrow U(1)^{r}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})

(15.3.3)

Therefore, the low energy effective theory consists of
r
copies of
{\cal N}=2
electro-magnetism with compact
U(1)
gauge groups, up to identification under the Weyl group
{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
.

The field contents of the low energy theory consists of
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplets with component fields
(A_{\mu}^{I},\lambda_{\alpha}^{I},\tilde{\lambda}_{\alpha}^{I},\phi^{I})
for gauge group
U(1)^{r}
and
I=1,\cdots,r
which may be decomposed into
{\cal N}=1
gauge superfields
V^{I}
and chiral superfields
\Phi^{I}
. The theory is governed by the Lagrangian constructed in (
15.2.3
) for gauge group
U(1)^{r}
,

\displaystyle{\cal L}=\int d^{4}\theta\,K\big(\Phi,\Phi^{\dagger}\big)+{\rm Re\,}\int d^{2}\theta\,\tau_{IJ}\,W^{I}W^{J}

(15.3.4)

The field strength superfields
W^{I}
of the Abelian gauge multiplets simplifies,

\displaystyle W^{I}_{\alpha}=-{1\over 4}\bar{D}\bar{D}D_{\alpha}V^{I}

(15.3.5)

and
\tau_{IJ}(\Phi)
is holomorphic in the fields
\Phi^{I}
. Since the chiral fields
\Phi^{I}
are neutral under the gauge group
U(1)^{r}
the factor
e^{V}
multiplying
\Phi
in (
15.2.3
) is absent here. No superpotential
U(\Phi^{I})
is allowed in view of
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry, as may be shown by the same argument used earlier in the case of renormalizable Lagrangians. Expressed in terms of component fields the Lagrangian takes the form,
29
29

29

We use the standard notation for coordinates on Kähler manifolds where
\phi^{\bar{I}}=\bar{\phi}^{I}
so that the metric is
g_{I\bar{J}}\,d\phi^{I}\otimes d\phi^{\bar{J}}
. The Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma
for the metric
g_{I\bar{J}}
is compatible with the complex structure so that its mixed components
\Gamma^{I}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}},\,\Gamma^{I}_{J\bar{K}}
and their complex conjugates vanish. The corresponding covariant derivatives are given by
D_{\mu}f^{I}=\partial_{\mu}f^{I}+\Gamma^{I}_{JK}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{J}\,f^{K}
for the fields
f^{I}=\phi^{I},\lambda^{I},\tilde{\lambda}^{I}
.

\displaystyle{\cal L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{I\bar{J}}(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Big(D_{\mu}\phi^{I}D^{\mu}\phi^{\bar{J}}+i\overline{\tilde{\lambda}}^{\bar{J}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\tilde{\lambda}^{I}\Big)

(15.3.6)

\displaystyle-{\rm Re\,}\left\{\tau_{IJ}(\phi)\left({i\over 2}F_{\mu\nu}^{I}F^{J\mu\nu}-{1\over 2}F^{I}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{J\mu\nu}+\bar{\lambda}^{I}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\lambda^{J}\right)\right\}

The
SU(2)_{R}
symmetry of
{\cal N}=2
rotates
\lambda^{I}
into
\tilde{\lambda}^{I}
so that invariance of
{\cal L}
requires the following relation between their coefficients
\tau_{IJ}
and the Kähler metric
g_{I\bar{J}}
.

\displaystyle{\rm Im\,}\tau_{IJ}(\phi)=g_{I\bar{J}}(\phi,\bar{\phi})={\partial^{2}K(\phi,\bar{\phi})\over\partial\phi^{I}\partial\phi^{\bar{J}}}

(15.3.7)

Since
\tau_{IJ}(\phi)
is holomorphic, this relation implies that the Kähler potential
K
must be of the special Kähler type, so that
\tau_{IJ}(\phi)
and
K
are given by,

\displaystyle\tau_{IJ}(\phi)={\partial^{2}{\cal F}(\phi)\over\partial\phi^{I}\partial\phi^{J}}\hskip 43.36243ptK(\phi,\bar{\phi})={1\over 2i}\left(\bar{\phi}^{I}\,\phi_{DI}-\phi^{I}\,\bar{\phi}_{DI}\right)\hskip 36.135pt\phi_{DI}={\partial{\cal F}(\phi)\over\partial\phi^{I}}

(15.3.8)

where
{\cal F}(\phi)
is a locally holomorphic function of
\phi^{I}
referred to as the pre-potential. Since the pre-potential
{\cal F}
depends only on the field
\phi
and not on any derivatives of
\phi
, its expression is entirely determined by evaluating
{\cal F}
on the vacuum expectation values of
\phi
, given in (
15.3.2
).

15.4

BPS states, monopoles, and dyons

In addition to the massless states described by the canonical fields of the
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplet, the theory also contains the massive gauge boson states as well as the ‘t Hooft-Polyakov magnetic monopoles produced by the Higgs mechanism
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
. These states are BPS states and carry non-zero electric and/or magnetic charges under the various
\mathfrak{u}(1)
fields. In this subsection we shall review the properties of these states.

The massive vector bosons, and their superpartners, that arise via the Higgs mechanism from the spontaneous symmetry breaking
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
correspond to the root generators
\mathfrak{e}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
in the decomposition
30
30

30

Our notation for Cartan generators and roots for a rank
r
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
in the Cartan basis is as follows. The Cartan generators will be denoted
\mathfrak{h}_{I}
with
I=1,\cdots,r
, as we have already used in (
15.3.2
) when parametrizing the vacua of the Coulomb branch. The root generators are denoted
\mathfrak{e}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, where the corresponding root vector is an
r
-dimensional vector with components
\alpha_{I}
. Their commutators may now be expressed as follows,
[\mathfrak{h}_{I},\mathfrak{h}_{J}]=0
,
[\mathfrak{h}_{I},\mathfrak{e}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}]=\alpha_{I}\mathfrak{e}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, and
[\mathfrak{e}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\mathfrak{e}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}]=\sum_{I}\alpha_{I}\mathfrak{h}_{I}
.
of the generators of
\mathfrak{g}
while the massless gauge bosons of
\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
correspond to the Cartan generators
\mathfrak{h}_{I}
identified in (
15.3.2
). For each root
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
there is a unique massive vector boson multiplet in the spectrum. Counting the number of states for each spin, we see that these massive vector bosons must belong to BPS multiplets. As a result, the BPS formula gives their masses in terms of their central charges,

\displaystyle M^{W}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot{\bf a}|\hskip 72.26999pt{\bf a}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{r})

(15.4.1)

a formula that is a direct consequence of the Higgs mechanism by a scalar
\phi
in the adjoint representation of the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

The massive magnetic monopole states, and their superpartners, also arise via the Higgs mechanism
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{u}(1)^{r}
as ‘t Hooft-Polyakov magnetic monopoles. In the classical limit, they arise as static solutions of finite mass that saturate the Bogomolnyi bound,

\displaystyle M={2\over g^{2}}\left|\int d^{3}x\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot{\rm tr}\big(\phi\,{\bf B}^{I})\right|

(15.4.2)

where
{\bf B}^{I}
is the magnetic field component of the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}^{I}
and the integral gives the magnetic charge of the field
F^{I}_{\mu\nu}
. Precisely one magnetic monopole arises for each possible embedding of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
into
\mathfrak{g}
, i.e. for each root
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. This construction will give us a BPS formula (at the semi-classical level) for
the mass of the ‘t Hooft-Polyakov magnetic monopole in terms of the vacuum expectation value of the dual gauge scalar
\phi_{DI}
. Applying this construction for each
SU(2)
subgroup of
G
, namely for each root of
\mathfrak{g}
, we find the semi-classical magnetic monopole BPS mass formula,

\displaystyle M^{M}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=|\boldsymbol{\beta}\cdot{\bf a}_{D}|\hskip 72.26999pt{\bf a}_{D}=(a_{D1},\cdots,a_{Dr})\,.

(15.4.3)

Here, we have also included the effect of the
\theta
angle, which is to produce an electric charge on the magnetic monopole and make it into a dyon.

More generally, a dyon may be specified by two roots: one root
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
for its electric charge as in the construction of vector boson masses, and one (co)root
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
for its magnetic charge as in the construction of the magnetic monopole masses. The full quantum BPS mass formula for such a dyon is,

\displaystyle M^{D}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\big|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot{\bf a}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\cdot{\bf a}_{D}\big|

(15.4.4)

The theory further contains an infinite number of states that may be neutral or carry arbitrary electric and magnetic charges, but that do not saturate the BPS bound and are thus non-BPS states. No simple formula for their mass in terms of the other quantum numbers is known to exist. Henceforth, we restrict attention to the BPS spectrum only.

15.5

SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-duality of the
{\cal N}=4
theory

For the
{\cal N}=4
theory, the pre-potential is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal F}(\phi)={1\over 2}\tau\sum_{I}\phi^{I}\phi^{I}

(15.5.1)

so that
a_{DI}=\tau a_{I}
and
\tau_{IJ}=\tau\delta_{IJ}
with
\tau
independent of
\phi
. The spectrum of vector boson, magnetic monopole, and dyon BPS states is then given by,

\displaystyle M^{D}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(\tau)=\big|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot{\bf a}+\tau{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\cdot{\bf a}\big|

(15.5.2)

The roots
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
may be decomposed onto a basis of simple roots
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{I}
,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\sum_{I}n_{I}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{I}\hskip 72.26999pt{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\sum_{I}m_{I}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{I}

(15.5.3)

where the coefficients
m_{I},n_{I}
are integers. The masses of the BPS states are then given by,

\displaystyle M_{m,n}(\tau)=\big|Z\big|\hskip 72.26999ptZ=\sum_{I}(n_{I}+m_{I}\tau)\,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{I}\cdot{\bf a}

(15.5.4)

One of the original indications of the
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
Montonen-Olive duality symmetry of the maximally supersymmetric
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theory was that the spectrum of BPS states is invariant under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations of the complexified coupling
\tau
,

\displaystyle M_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})=M_{m,n}(\tau)\hskip 43.36243pt\tau\to\tau^{\prime}={a\tau+b\over c\tau+d}\hskip 43.36243pt{\bf a}\to{\bf a}^{\prime}={{\bf a}\over c\tau+d}

(15.5.5)

provided the states are mapped as follows,

\displaystyle m_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle am_{I}^{\prime}+cn_{I}^{\prime}

\displaystyle n_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle bm_{I}^{\prime}+dn_{I}^{\prime}

(15.5.6)

We note, however, that
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
does not in general map
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills with gauge group
G
to itself. Instead, as we shall now explain, it transforms between theories with the same Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
but different global structures.

15.5.1

The global structure of
{\cal N}=4
theories

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of an
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theory does not uniquely specify the theory. Rather there remain different choices for the global structure of the theory, including (but not limited to) the particular choice of gauge group
G
corresponding to the algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
31
31

31

Throughout this section, we shall restrict attention to gauge groups that are connected and compact. An example in which the global structure of the gauge group alone is not enough to fully specify the global structure is provided by the case of
SO(3)
super Yang-Mills, which will be discussed below.
The various Lie groups associated with a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
may be obtained by first identifying the unique simply-connected group
{\cal G}
whose Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{g}
, and then identifying the center
Z({\cal G})
of
{\cal G}
. All of the other gauge groups corresponding to the algebra
\mathfrak{g}
are then of the form
G_{H}={\cal G}/H
for
H
a subgroup of
Z({\cal G})
.
Whenever
H
is non-trivial, i.e. contains at least one element other than the identity, the group
G_{H}
is non-simply connected and a standard argument shows that its first homotopy group
\pi_{1}(G_{H})
is isomorphic to
H
.

The weight lattice
\Lambda_{w}
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is the lattice spanned by the weights of all possible finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{g}
, and coincides with the weight lattice of the simply connected Lie group
{\cal G}
so that
\Lambda_{w}=\Lambda_{w}^{\cal G}
. The weight lattice of a non-simply connected subgroup
G_{H}\subset{\cal G}
will be denoted
\Lambda_{w}^{G_{H}}
. Every representation of
G_{H}
is a representation of
{\cal G}
so that
\Lambda_{w}^{G/H}\subset\Lambda_{w}^{\cal G}
but the converse is not true, so that the inclusion is a strict one whenever
H
is non-trivial. The root lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
is a sub-lattice of
\Lambda_{w}^{G/H}
for every
H\subset Z({\cal G})
.

We shall now consider the
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theories with gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and gauge group
G=G_{H}
for the different choices of
H
. An
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformation maps the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
to itself, and maps the gauge group
G_{H}
to a gauge group
G_{H^{\prime}}
where
H^{\prime}
is not necessarily equal to
H
. This means that the allowed representations of
G_{H}
and
G_{H^{\prime}}
will differ from one another when
H^{\prime}\not=H
and is most easily seen by analyzing the spectrum of line operators in each theory. These line operators come in the following types:

•

Wilson lines:
To each center subgroup
H
and corresponding gauge group
G_{H}
is associated a set of Wilson lines, which correspond physically to the worldlines of heavy probe particles that transform under a representation
{\cal R}
of
G_{H}
. The Wilson line may be expressed as the holonomy of the gauge field
A
in the representation
{\cal R}
of
G_{H}
,

\displaystyle W_{\cal R}(\gamma)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\cal R}\left(P\exp\Big\{i\oint_{\gamma}A\Big\}\right)

(15.5.7)

with
\gamma
a 1-cycle in the four-dimensional spacetime. Defined in this way, each Wilson line is manifestly invariant under
H
since
A
is invariant, but the selection of the representation
{\cal R}
of
G_{H}
depends on
H
, as explained earlier. Note that since Wilson lines are worldlines of probe particles, their existence does not depend on the dynamical matter content of the theory: once the gauge group
G_{H}
is fixed, Wilson lines will exist for every representation
{\cal R}
of
G_{H}
. In terms of the weight lattice
\Lambda_{w}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, the Wilson lines correspond to points in
\Lambda^{G_{H}}_{w}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
, with
\Lambda_{w}^{G_{H}}\subset\Lambda_{w}
the sub-lattice of weights of
G_{H}
and
{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
.

•

’t Hooft lines:
Gauge theories also come equipped with ’t Hooft lines, which are the magnetic counterparts of the Wilson lines. Unlike Wilson lines, they cannot be written as local functionals of the fields of the gauge theory, but they are labelled in an analogous way. In particular, let us denote the magnetic dual (also referred to as the Langlands or Goddard-Nuyts-Olive (GNO) dual) algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. The algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
is dual to
\mathfrak{g}
in the sense that the weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, which we denote by
\Lambda_{w}^{\vee}
, is the dual of the root lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
. For simply-laced
\mathfrak{g}
we have
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, whereas for non-simply-laced algebras one has

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{r}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{c}_{r}\hskip 36.135pt\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{b}_{r}\hskip 36.135pt\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{g}_{2}\hskip 36.135pt\mathfrak{f}_{4}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{f}_{4}

(15.5.8)

in the standard Cartan notation in which
\mathfrak{b}_{r},\mathfrak{c}_{r},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{f}_{4}
are the Lie algebras of the Lie groups
SO(2r+1)
,
Sp(2r)
,
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
, respectively. At the level of groups the correspondence is more subtle, with the results collected in Table
2
. Note that,

\displaystyle{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})={\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})\hskip 72.26999ptZ({\cal G}^{\vee})=Z({\cal G})

(15.5.9)

The ’t Hooft lines are then labelled by a particular subset of the points in
\Lambda_{w}^{\vee}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
, specified by the global structure of the magnetic gauge group
G^{\vee}
. The way to determine this subset will be explained below.

•

Dyonic lines:
Finally, we can consider mixed Wilson and ’t Hooft lines, labelled by a subset of the electric and magnetic charges,

\displaystyle(q_{e},q_{m})\in\Lambda_{w}\times\Lambda_{w}^{\vee}

(15.5.10)

subject to the identification,

\displaystyle(q_{e},\,q_{m})\sim(wq_{e},\,wq_{m})\hskip 36.135ptw\in{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})

(15.5.11)

Note that this set can be larger than the set of pairs of representations of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, which is labelled instead by
(\Lambda_{w}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g}))\times(\Lambda_{w}^{\vee}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g}))
.

G

G^{\vee}

SU(NM)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}

SU(NM)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}

SO(2N)

SO(2N)

Spin(2N)

N
odd

Spin(2N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}

Spin(2N)

N
even

SO(2N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

SO(2N+1)

USp(2N)

Spin(2N+1)

USp(2N)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

E_{6}

E_{6}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}

E_{7}

E_{7}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

Table 2
:
Langlands or GNO dual groups
G^{\vee}
for various gauge groups
G
, with
(G^{\vee})^{\vee}=G
. The gauge groups which do not appear in this table, namely
G_{2},F_{4},
and
E_{8}
, have trivial center and hence only a single global form, meaning that
G^{\vee}=G
.

We now explain how to determine the subset of allowed ’t Hooft and dyonic lines. Having fixed a global form
G_{H}
for the gauge group, we know that the spectrum of Wilson lines is the set of all lines
(q_{e},0)
with
q_{e}\in\Lambda_{w}^{G_{H}}/{\cal W}(\mathfrak{g})
. The spectrum of ’t Hooft and dyonic lines is then given by the full spectrum of lines which are mutually local to the Wilson lines. By mutual locality, we mean that if one computes the correlation function of two lines supported on
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, and then moves
\gamma^{\prime}
in a loop around
\gamma
, the correlation function is unchanged.

To make this more concrete, note that independent of the particular global group
G_{H}
, Wilson lines labelled by roots of
\mathfrak{g}
must always be present, since roots are elements of
\Lambda^{G_{H}}_{w}
for any choice of subgroup
H
. Likewise, even without solving the mutual locality constraint, it is clear that we must have at least the ’t Hooft lines labelled by roots of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. Because the charge lattice is closed under addition and inversion—i.e. if
(q_{e},q_{m})
is present then so is
(-q_{e},-q_{m})
, and if furthermore
(q_{e}^{\prime},q_{m}^{\prime})
is present then so is
(q_{e}+q_{e}^{\prime},q_{m}+q_{m}^{\prime})
—it is useful to organize lines into families labelled by the weight lattice modulo the root lattice. The weight lattice modulo the root lattice is nothing but the center, and recalling that
Z({\cal G})=Z({\cal G}^{\vee})
we conclude that we may label families of lines by pairs

\displaystyle(z_{e},z_{m})\in Z({\cal G})\times Z({\cal G})

(15.5.12)

If one element in such a family exists, so do all of the other elements, obtained by adding arbitrary root vectors.

For charges valued in the center, the mutual locality condition mentioned above takes a simple form. For
Z({\cal G})={\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
, which is the case for all simple Lie groups except for
SO(4N+2)
,
32
32

32

In the case of
SO(4N+2)
the center is
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. This case is more complicated and will not be discussed here; see
[
283
]
for details.
then for lines
(z_{e},z_{m})
and
(z_{e}^{\prime},z_{m}^{\prime})
the mutual locality constraint reads
[
283
]
,

\displaystyle z_{e}z_{m}^{\prime}-z_{m}z_{e}^{\prime}=0\,\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,N

(15.5.13)

We note that even upon fixing the global form of the gauge group
G_{H}
, and hence the lattice of allowed electric charges
z_{e}
, there can be multiple solutions to the mutual locality constraint, giving different lattices of dyonic lines. To fully specify the
{\cal N}=4
theory, one must specify all of this additional global data.

15.5.2

Example:
\mathfrak{su}(2)
SYM

Let us illustrate the above through the example of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
. In this case
{\cal G}=SU(2)
and
Z({\cal G})={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. If we take
H\subset Z({\cal G})
to be trivial, then
G_{H}=SU(2)
itself. Aside from the identity line
(z_{e},z_{m})=(0,0)
, the spectrum of Wilson lines is generated by elements of the non-trivial center, which is just
(z_{e},z_{m})=(1,0)
. The mutual locality condition requires that any other family of lines labelled by
(z_{e}^{\prime},z_{m}^{\prime})
satisfies
z_{m}^{\prime}=0\,\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,2
, and hence that no other non-trivial representation of the center is allowed. The lattice of charges for
G_{H}=SU(2)
is shown in the gray square in the leftmost part of Figure
16
—the full lattice is obtained from this by adding elements of the root lattice.

q_{e}
q_{m}
q_{e}
q_{m}
q_{e}
q_{m}

Figure 16
:
Lattice of line operators for the
SU(2)
,
SO(3)_{+}
, and
SO(3)_{-}
theories. The red dots represent occupied lattice points. The grey square represents the sublattice of elements
(z_{e},z_{m})\in Z(SU(2))\times Z(SU(2))
valued in the center, which can be used to obtain the entire lattice by adding arbitrary root vectors.

We can instead choose
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, in which case
G_{H}=SU(2)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=SO(3)
. The Wilson lines are now labelled by representations of
SO(3)
, which have even electric charges, and hence which, when reduced to the center, are all trivial
(z_{e},z_{m})=(0,0)
. Because of this, demanding mutual locality with Wilson lines does not impose any constraints on the spectrum of ’t Hooft and dyonic lines operators, and we may choose to have either
(z_{e},z_{m})=(0,1)
or
(1,1)
occupied (but not both, since the two are not mutually local). This gives the middle and right lattices in Figure
16
. We note here that even upon fixing the gauge group
G_{H}=SO(3)
, there are still two distinct choices for the spectrum of line operators, and one must specify the full lattice of lines before one completely specifies the theory. The two theories in this case are typically denoted by
SO(3)_{+}
and
SO(3)_{-}
.

Let us point out here that the only ingredient entering in the above analysis was the center of
{\cal G}
. We may thus conclude that the case of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{e}_{7}
, for which the center is again
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, is exactly identical, again with three distinct global variants. For groups with larger centers the analysis is more involved, but conceptually straightforward. On the other hand, when the center is trivial as for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{e}_{8}
, there is only a single variant of the theory.

15.5.3

SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on line operators

Having understood the spectrum of line operators, we may now return to the question of the action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The
S
and
T
transformations act in a straightforward way on the spectrum of line operators,

\displaystyle S:(q_{e},q_{m})\rightarrow(q_{m},q_{e})\hskip 36.135ptT:(q_{e},q_{m})\rightarrow(q_{e}+q_{m},q_{m})

(15.5.14)

The first of these is the famous result that the
S
transformation implements electro-magnetic duality or so-called Montonen-Olive duality on the theory, while the second is the effect by which magnetic monopoles become dyonic upon shift of the theta angle.

In the context of the example of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
, examining the action of
S
and
T
on the charge lattices in Figure
16
reveals that

\displaystyle S:\hskip 14.45377ptSU(2)~

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle SO(3)_{+}\hskip 43.36243ptT:\hskip 16.62178ptSU(2)\,\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\,SU(2)

\displaystyle SO(3)_{+}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle SU(2)\hskip 79.49744ptSO(3)_{+}\,\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\,SO(3)_{-}

\displaystyle SO(3)_{-}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle SO(3)_{-}\hskip 72.26999ptSO(3)_{-}\,\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\,SO(3)_{+}

(15.5.15)

Thus we see that no individual theory is actually self-dual under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Instead, one can think of the set of three theories as transforming as a three-dimensional vector-valued modular function of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Alternatively, each individual theory can be said to be invariant under an appropriate congruence subgroup. For example, for
G_{H}=SU(2)
the theory is invariant under
T
and
ST^{2}S
, which together generate the subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(2)\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Thus the
SU(2)
theory is self-dual under
\Gamma_{0}(2)
.

Let us close by mentioning that, in the cases in which there is only a single global variant of the theory, e.g. for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{e}_{8}
mentioned above, then the full
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
really does act as a duality group for the theory.

15.6

The Seiberg-Witten Solution

The Seiberg-Witten solution for an
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory in four space-time dimensions with gauge group
G
of rank
r
, is given in terms of the vacuum expectation values of the complex scalars
a_{I}
of the gauge multiplet and their magnetic duals
a_{DI}
in the Coulomb phase. When hypermultiplets are present in a representation
{\cal R}\oplus\bar{\cal R}
of the gauge group
G
, we shall denote their
G
-invariant masses by
m_{f}
where the index
f
runs over the various irreducible components
{\cal R}_{f}
of
{\cal R}
. The prepotential
{\cal F}
and the matrix
\tau_{IJ}
for
I,J=1,\cdots,r
of effective couplings and mixings of the unbroken
U(1)^{r}
gauge multiplets are related to these expectation values by,

\displaystyle a_{DI}={\partial{\cal F}\over\partial a_{I}}\hskip 72.26999pt\tau_{IJ}={\partial a_{DI}\over\partial a_{J}}={\partial^{2}{\cal F}\over\partial a_{I}\,\partial a_{J}}

(15.6.1)

The original Seiberg-Witten solution was obtained for gauge group
SU(2)
, but the results were quickly generalized to other semi-simple compact gauge groups
G
.

The building blocks of the Seiberg-Witten solution are a curve
{\cal C}
and a differential
\lambda
on
{\cal C}
. More precisely,
{\cal C}={\cal C}(u,m)
is a family of genus
r
Riemann surfaces parametrized by
G
-invariant local complex coordinates
u_{1},\cdots u_{r}
and hypermultiplet masses
m_{f}
. A convenient definition of the
u_{I}
may be given in terms of the gauge-invariant traces
{\rm tr}(\phi^{n})
of the gauge scalar
\phi
. The differential
\lambda
is meromorphic on
{\cal C}
and its partial derivatives with respect to the parameters
u_{I}
are holomorphic Abelian differentials, possibly up to the addition of exact differentials of meromorphic functions on
{\cal C}
. In the presence of hypermultiplets,
\lambda
has poles whose residues are given by the masses
m_{f}
.

The Seiberg-Witten solution gives the vacuum expectation values of the gauge scalars
a_{I}
and their magnetic duals
a_{DI}
in terms of the periods of the differential
\lambda
on a canonical basis of homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
of the curve
{\cal C}
,

\displaystyle 2\pi ia_{I}=\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}\lambda\hskip 72.26999pt2\pi ia_{DI}=\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}\lambda

(15.6.2)

By the very construction of this set-up, the matrix
\tau_{IJ}
has positive imaginary part as required on physical grounds by the fact that it provides the couplings for the
U(1)^{r}
gauge field strengths
F_{\mu\nu}^{I}
, which must be positive. To see this, we combine the following formulas,

\displaystyle\tau_{IJ}=\sum_{K}{\partial a_{DI}\over\partial u_{K}}{\partial u_{K}\over\partial a_{J}}\hskip 43.36243pt2\pi i{\partial a_{I}\over\partial u_{K}}=\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}{\partial\lambda\over\partial u_{K}}\hskip 43.36243pt2\pi i{\partial a_{DI}\over\partial u_{K}}=\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}{\partial\lambda\over\partial u_{K}}

(15.6.3)

to observe that
\tau_{IJ}
is the period matrix of the curve
{\cal C}(u)
, whose imaginary part is positive by the Riemann bilinear relations; see appendix
B.1.5
.

To illustrate the general construction outlined above, we present the Seiberg-Witten curves and differentials for the gauge group
G=SU(N)
in two examples. The first has
N_{f}<2N
hypermultiplets with masses
m_{f}
in the defining representation of
G
and is asymptotically free. The second has one hypermultiplet with mass
m
in the adjoint representation of
G
and is referred to as the
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory. The latter is UV finite and reduces to the
{\cal N}=4
super Yang-Mills theory upon setting the mass
m
of the adjoint hypermultiplet to zero, and to the pure
{\cal N}=2
theory without hypermultiplets by sending the mass
m
to infinity while suitably scaling the gauge-coupling to zero.

15.6.1

The
N_{f}<2N
theory for gauge group
SU(N)

For the theory with
N_{f}<2N
hypermultiplets in the fundamental representation of
SU(N)
with masses
m_{f}
, the differential
\lambda
and the curve
{\cal C}
are given by,

\displaystyle\lambda=\left(A^{\prime}-{1\over 2}(A-y){B^{\prime}\over B}\right){xdx\over y}\hskip 72.26999pty^{2}=A(x)^{2}-B(x)

(15.6.4)

where the polynomials
A
and
B
are given as follows,

\displaystyle A(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(x-\bar{a}_{i})\hskip 72.26999ptB(x)=\Lambda^{2N-N_{f}}\prod_{f=1}^{N_{f}}(x-m_{f})

(15.6.5)

and
\Lambda
is the renormalization scale of the asymptotically free theory. The gauge-invariant coordinates
u_{I}
are given in terms of symmetric polynomials in the variables
\bar{a}_{i}
which, since the gauge group is
SU(N)
, must satisfy
\sum_{i}\bar{a}_{i}=0
. The curve
{\cal C}
is hyperelliptic of genus
N-1
. The differential
\lambda
has simple poles with residue
m_{f}
at the points
(x,y)=(m_{f},-A(m_{f}))
on the second sheet of the hyper-elliptic curve, but is regular at the points
(x,y)=(m_{f},A(m_{f}))
on the first sheet of
{\cal C}
.

15.7

The
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory for gauge group
SU(N)

The
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory possesses a rich modular structure, in part because it may be obtained as a mass deformation from the
{\cal N}=4
theory, shares the same ultraviolet finiteness properties, and has a marginal deformation controlled by the complex gauge coupling
\tau
. Its Seiberg-Witten theory may be developed in terms of integrable mechanical systems, of the Hitchin or elliptic Calogero-Moser type. It is the latter approach that we shall follow here.

15.7.1

The Seiberg-Witten solution

To formulate the Seiberg-Witten solution for the
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory for gauge group
SU(N)
, we introduce a torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
for a lattice
\Lambda=\omega_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\omega_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
where the modulus
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
is related to the gauge coupling
g
and the
\theta
angle of the non-Abelian gauge theory by,

\displaystyle\tau={\theta\over 2\pi}+{4\pi i\over g^{2}}

(15.7.1)

Denoting the mass of the adjoint hypermultiplet by
m
, the differential
\lambda
and the curve
{\cal C}
are constructed in terms of a local complex coordinate
z
on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\lambda=k\,dz\hskip 72.26999ptR_{N}(k,z|\Lambda)=0

(15.7.2)

The function
R_{N}(k,z|\Lambda)
is given by the characteristic polynomial for the Lax operator of the elliptic Calogero-Moser system for the root system
\mathfrak{a}_{N-1}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(N)
,

\displaystyle R_{N}(k,z|\Lambda)={\rm det\,}\big(kI-L(z|\Lambda)\big)

(15.7.3)

The components of the matrix
L(z|\Lambda)
and its conjugate Lax matrix
M(z|\Lambda)
are given by,

\displaystyle L_{ij}(z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{i}\delta_{ij}-m(1-\delta_{ij})\Phi(x_{i}-x_{j},z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle M_{ij}(z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(1-\delta_{ij})\Phi^{\prime}(x_{i}-x_{j},z|\Lambda)+m\delta_{ij}\sum_{k\not=i}\wp(x_{i}-x_{k}|\Lambda)

(15.7.4)

where
\wp
is the Weierstrass function and
\Phi
is the Lamé function,

\displaystyle\Phi(x,z|\Lambda)={\sigma(z-x|\Lambda)\over\sigma(z|\Lambda)\sigma(x|\Lambda)}\,e^{x\zeta(z|\Lambda)}

(15.7.5)

given in terms of the Weierstrass
\zeta
- and
\sigma
-functions for the lattice
\Lambda
; see subsection
2.4.3
for definitions of
\zeta
and
\sigma
. The variables
x_{i}
and
p_{i}
are the positions and momenta for the Calogero-Moser system. The integrability condition
\dot{L}=[M,L]
guarantees that
R_{N}(k,z)
is a conserved quantity for all values of
z,k
, and
\Lambda
.

15.7.2

The Seiberg-Witten curve

The curve
R(k,z|\Lambda)
admits an explicit presentation directly in terms of
\vartheta
-functions,

\displaystyle R_{N}(k,z|\Lambda)=\vartheta_{1}\left({z\over\omega_{1}}\bigg|\tau\right)^{-1}\vartheta_{1}\left({z\over\omega_{1}}-m{\partial\over\partial\tilde{k}}\bigg|\tau\right)P(\tilde{k})\bigg|_{\tilde{k}=k+mh_{1}(z|\Lambda)}

(15.7.6)

Here
P(k)
is a monic polynomial in
k
of degree
N
with vanishing
k^{N-1}
term, reflecting the fact that the gauge group is
SU(N)
as opposed to
U(N)
,

\displaystyle P(k)=k^{N}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}u_{n}k^{n}

(15.7.7)

The
N-1
remaining independent coefficients
u_{n}
are the moduli of the Coulomb branch. The functions
h_{n}
are defined by,

\displaystyle h_{n}(z|\Lambda)=\vartheta_{1}\left({z\over\omega_{1}}\bigg|\tau\right)^{-1}{\partial^{n}\over\partial z^{n}}\vartheta_{1}\left({z\over\omega_{1}}\bigg|\tau\right)

(15.7.8)

One may think of the roots of the polynomial as the vacuum expectation values of the gauge scalars
a_{I}
when
m=0
. The corresponding curves for
N=2,3,4
are given by,
33
33

33

The Weierstrass function
\wp(z|\tau)
differs from
h_{2}(z|\Lambda)-h_{1}(z|\Lambda)^{2}
by a
z
-independent term given by

\wp(z|\Lambda)=-\partial_{z}^{2}\ln\vartheta_{1}(z/\omega_{1}|\tau)-{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)/(3\omega_{1}^{2})
, as may be established using (
4.1.7
). In the final expressions given in (
15.7.9
) we have used the freedom to redefine the moduli parameters
u_{n}
by
\Lambda
-dependent but
z
-independent shifts, such as for example
u_{0}\to u_{0}-m^{2}\,{\sf E}_{2}/(3\omega_{1}^{2})
for
N=2
. These shifts are immaterial as the
u_{n}
provide an arbitrary coordinate system for the moduli of the Coulomb branch. The resulting shifted versions of (
15.7.9
) have the advantage of enjoying transparent modular transformation properties.

\displaystyle R_{2}(k,z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{2}+u_{0}-m^{2}\wp

(15.7.9)

\displaystyle R_{3}(k,z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{3}+(u_{1}-3m^{2}\wp)k+u_{0}+m^{3}\wp^{\prime}

\displaystyle R_{4}(k,z|\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{4}+(u_{2}-6m^{2}\wp)k^{2}+(u_{1}+4m^{3}\wp^{\prime})k+u_{0}-m^{4}\wp^{\prime\prime}+3m^{4}\wp^{2}

The SW curve may thus be constructed from
N
copies, or sheets, of the underlying torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
, namely one for each solution
k_{I}
, glued together wherever two sheets
I\not=J
intersect one another. A schematic representation of the underlying torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
and the curve
{\cal C}(u)
for gauge group
SU(3)
is presented in Figure
17
.

A canonical choice for
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
cycles on the torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
may be uplifted to a canonical homology basis of cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{I}
for
I=1,\cdots,N-1
on the sheet corresponding to the solution
k_{I}
, as illustrated for
N=3
in Figure
17
. The modular group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
acts on the cycles
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
by,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{B}\cr\mathfrak{A}\end{matrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\cr\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{B}\cr\mathfrak{A}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(15.7.10)

and this transformation may be lifted to a transformation on the homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
on the curves
{\cal C}(u)
by a subgroup
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
of the full modular group of the curve
Sp(2N-2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. However, independent of this induced action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
, we may act on the cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{I}
of the curve
{\cal C}(u)
by the full modular group
Sp(2N-2,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Thus, the action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
and the action of
Sp(2N-2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on
\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{I}
may be viewed as independent of one another, producing a larger modular structure governed by the group,

\displaystyle SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})\ltimes Sp(2N-2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(15.7.11)

The corresponding construction gives rise to the notion of bi-modular forms, and may be extended to the superconformal
{\cal N}=2
theory with gauge group
SU(N)
and
2N
hypermultiplets in the fundamental representation of
SU(N)
.

\mathfrak{A}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
{\cal C}(u)
\mathfrak{A}_{3}
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
\mathfrak{A}
\mathfrak{B}
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda

Figure 17
:
Schematic representation of the Seiberg-Witten curve
{\cal C}(u)
and the underlying torus
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
for gauge group
SU(3)
. Canonical homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}
on
{\cal C}(u)
may be provided by uplifting the cycles
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
of a canonical homology basis on
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
. The identifications between the different sheets are not shown here.

15.7.3

Gauge scalar vacuum expectation values and pre-potential

In terms of these data, the expectation values
a_{I}
and their magnetic duals
a_{DI}
are given by,

\displaystyle 2\pi ia_{I}=\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}k\,dz=\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}k_{I}dz\hskip 72.26999pt2\pi ia_{DI}=\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}k\,dz=\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}k_{I}dz

(15.7.12)

The pre-potential
{\cal F}
satisfies the following renormalization-type differential equation in
\tau
,

\displaystyle{\partial{\cal F}\over\partial\tau}\bigg|_{m,a_{I}}={1\over 4\pi i}\sum_{I=1}^{N-1}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}dz\,k^{2}={1\over 4\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz\sum_{I=1}^{N-1}k_{I}^{2}

(15.7.13)

The partial derivative is considered at fixed
m
and
a_{I}
. Note that
\partial{\cal F}/\partial\tau
receives no perturbative contributions, as those are independent of
\tau
. The advantage of this formula is that we can now evaluate
a_{I}
and
\partial{\cal F}/\partial\tau
entirely in terms of integrals over
\mathfrak{A}
-periods without having to carry out the rather complicated analysis of turning points and regularization required when evaluating the
\mathfrak{B}
-periods.

In the limit
m\to 0
, we recover the
{\cal N}=4
theory and we have
R(k,z|\tau)=P(k)
, so that the roots of
P(k)
correspond to the vacuum expectation values of the gauge scalar of the
{\cal N}=2
gauge multiplet. Since we have
\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz=\omega_{1}
by definition of these periods, we also have
2\pi ia_{I}=\omega_{1}k_{I}
in the
m\to 0
limits, and thus we set
\omega_{1}=2\pi i
.

15.8

The
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory for gauge group
SU(2)

It is instructive to analyze the action of the duality group
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
for the case of gauge group
SU(2)
where many formulas and transformation laws can be made completely explicit. The curve
{\cal C}
of (
15.7.9
) and differential
\lambda
may be parametrized as follows,

\displaystyle k^{2}=\kappa^{2}+m^{2}\wp(z|\Lambda)\hskip 72.26999pt\lambda=\sqrt{\kappa^{2}+m^{2}\wp(z|\Lambda)}\,dz

(15.8.1)

where we have set
u_{0}=-\kappa^{2}
for later convenience. As
z\to 0
, we have
k\sim\pm m/z
which produces the pole in the differential
\lambda
whose residue is the hypermultiplet mass, as expected on the basis of the general construction outlined above. To evaluate the periods
a
and
a_{D}
, we begin by evaluating the following elliptic integrals, using the relation
\wp(z|\Lambda)=-\zeta^{\prime}(z|\Lambda)
and the monodromy relations of
\zeta(z|\Lambda)
given in (
2.4.3
),

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz\,\wp(z|\Lambda)=-{2\,\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2}|\tau)\over(2\pi i)^{2}}={1\over 12}\,{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}dz\,\wp(z|\Lambda)=-{2\,\zeta(\tfrac{\tau}{2}|\tau)\over(2\pi i)^{2}}={\tau\over 12}\,{\sf E}_{2}(\tau)+{1\over 2\pi i}

(15.8.2)

where the last equality was obtained by combining the formulas (
2.6.21
), (
3.9.2
), (
3.9.7
), and (
4.1.6
). This immediately allows us to compute the right side of the RG equation (
15.7.13
),

\displaystyle{\partial{\cal F}\over\partial\tau}\bigg|_{m,a}={1\over 4\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dzk_{+}^{2}={1\over 4\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz\Big(\kappa^{2}+m^{2}\wp(z|\Lambda)\Big)={1\over 2}\kappa^{2}+{m^{2}\over 24}{\sf E}_{2}

(15.8.3)

15.8.1

Expansion in powers of the mass
m

To obtain the periods
a
and
a_{D}
, we expand the SW differential
\lambda
for
SU(2)
in powers of
m
,

\displaystyle\lambda=\kappa\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma(n-\tfrac{1}{2})\over\Gamma(-\tfrac{1}{2})n!}\left(-{m^{2}\over\kappa^{2}}\right)^{n}\wp(z|\Lambda)^{n}\,dz

(15.8.4)

This expansion will be uniformly convergent for small enough
m
provided
z
is kept away from
0
, which can always be achieved when we integrate over suitably chosen
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
cycles. To evaluate the periods
a
and
a_{D}
, we compute the integrals,

\displaystyle A_{n}={1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}}dz\,\wp(z|\Lambda)^{n}\hskip 72.26999ptB_{n}={1\over 2\pi i}\oint_{\mathfrak{B}}dz\,\wp(z|\Lambda)^{n}

(15.8.5)

Clearly, we have
A_{0}=1
and
B_{0}=\tau
while
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
were already evaluated in (
15.8
). For
n\geq 2
, we make use of the following recursion relation,

\displaystyle(8n-4)\wp^{n}=(2n-3)g_{2}\wp^{n-2}+(2n-4)g_{3}\wp^{n-3}+2\big(\wp^{\prime}\wp^{n-2}\big)^{\prime}

(15.8.6)

which is readily established using the defining equation
(\wp^{\prime})^{2}=4\wp^{3}-g_{2}\wp-g_{3}
. Eliminating
g_{2},g_{3}
in favor of
{\sf E}_{4},{\sf E}_{6}
for the period normalization
\omega_{1}=2\pi i
,

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle={4\pi^{4}\,{\sf E}_{4}\over 3\omega_{1}^{4}}={{\sf E}_{4}\over 12}

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle={8\pi^{6}\,{\sf E}_{6}\over 27\omega_{1}^{6}}=-{{\sf E}_{6}\over 216}

(15.8.7)

and integrating the relation (
15.8.6
), we obtain the following recursion relation, satisfied by both
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
with
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle(8n-4)A_{n}={2n-3\over 12}\,{\sf E}_{4}A_{n-2}-{n-2\over 108}\,{\sf E}_{6}A_{n-3}

(15.8.8)

The coefficients of this recursion relation are modular forms, but the initial conditions for
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
involve the quasi-modular form
{\sf E}_{2}
. Linearity of (
15.8.8
) guarantees that the solutions
A_{n},B_{n}
are linear in
{\sf E}_{2}
and may be cast in the following form,

\displaystyle A_{n}=K_{n}+{{\sf E}_{2}\over 12}\,L_{n-1}\hskip 72.26999ptB_{n}=\tau A_{n}+{L_{n-1}\over 2\pi i}

(15.8.9)

where
K_{n}
and
L_{n}
are modular forms of weight
2n
. The function
A_{n}
satisfies (
15.8.8
) given for
A_{n}
with initial conditions
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
, while
L_{n}
satisfies (
15.8.8
) for
B_{n}-\tau A_{n}
with initial conditions
B_{0}-\tau A_{0}=0
and
B_{1}-\tau A_{1}=(2\pi i)^{-1}
. Substituting these expressions into the expansion in powers of
m
of the period integrals, we obtain the
a
-period,

\displaystyle a=\kappa\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma(n-\tfrac{1}{2})\over\Gamma(-\tfrac{1}{2})n!}\left(-{m^{2}\over\kappa^{2}}\right)^{n}\left(K_{n}+{{\bf E}_{2}\over 12}\,L_{n-1}\right)

(15.8.10)

and the following result for the combination
a_{D}-\tau a
of the
a_{D}
and
a
periods,

\displaystyle a_{D}-\tau a={\kappa\over 2\pi i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma(n-\tfrac{1}{2})\over\Gamma(-\tfrac{1}{2})n!}\left(-{m^{2}\over\kappa^{2}}\right)^{n}L_{n-1}

(15.8.11)

We shall establish below that the combination
a_{D}-\tau a
of the periods transforms as a modular form in the modulus
\tau
.

15.8.2

Low orders and perturbative contribution

The Eisenstein series
{\sf E}_{2n}(\tau)
for
n\geq 1
are normalized to 1 at the cusp
\tau\to i\infty
and admit a series expansion at the cusp in powers of
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
. The following coefficients vanish,

\displaystyle K_{1}=L_{-1}=L_{1}=0

(15.8.12)

while the non-vanishing low order coefficients are given by,

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle={{\sf E}_{4}\over 144}

\displaystyle K_{3}

\displaystyle=-{{\sf E}_{6}\over 2160}

\displaystyle K_{4}

\displaystyle={5\,{\sf E}_{4}^{2}\over 48384}

\displaystyle K_{5}

\displaystyle=-{{\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}\over 77760}

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle={{\sf E}_{4}\over 80}

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=-{{\sf E}_{6}\over 1512}

\displaystyle L_{4}

\displaystyle={7\,{\sf E}_{4}^{2}\over 34560}

\displaystyle L_{5}

\displaystyle=-{29\,{\sf E}_{4}{\sf E}_{6}\over 1330560}

(15.8.13)

To solve for the prepotential, we may either integrate the RG equation, or integrate
a_{D}=\partial{\cal F}/\partial a
. In either case, we need to express
\kappa
as a function of
\tau,m,a
, which can be done order by order in
m
by inverting the series given in (
15.8.10
) for
a
as a function of
\tau,m,\kappa
,

\displaystyle\kappa=a\Bigg[1-{{\sf E}_{2}\over 6}{m^{2}\over(2a)^{2}}+{{\sf E}_{4}-2{\sf E}_{2}^{2}\over 72}{m^{4}\over(2a)^{4}}-{20{\sf E}_{2}^{3}-11{\sf E}_{2}{\sf E}_{4}-4{\sf E}_{6}\over 216}{m^{6}\over(2a)^{6}}+{\cal O}(m^{8})\bigg]\quad

(15.8.14)

The expression for
{\cal F}
derived from the RG equation is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal F}(\tau,m,a)={1\over 2}\tau a^{2}+{\cal F}_{0}(m,a)-{1\over 2\pi i}\bigg[{{\sf E}_{2}\over 24}{m^{4}\over(2a)^{2}}+\left({{\sf E}_{4}\over 720}+{{\sf E}_{2}^{2}\over 144}\right){m^{6}\over(2a)^{4}}\bigg]+{\cal O}(m^{8})

(15.8.15)

where
{\cal F}_{0}
is independent of
\tau
and undetermined by the RG equation. However, it may be determined from the expressions for
a_{D}
and
a
that we have already obtained, and we have,

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{0}(m,a)=cm^{2}+{m^{2}\over 4\pi i}\ln{a\over m}

(15.8.16)

In the weak coupling limit, where
q\to 0
and
E_{2},E_{4},E_{6}\to 1
we evaluate the effective gauge coupling
\tau_{{\rm IR}}
as follows,

\displaystyle\tau_{{\rm IR}}=\tau-{1\over\pi i}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n}\left({m^{2}\over 4a^{2}}\right)^{n}+{\cal O}(q)=\tau+{1\over\pi i}\ln\left(1-{m^{2}\over 4a^{2}}\right)+{\cal O}(q)

(15.8.17)

Thus, the perturbative logarithm at the mass of the massive vector boson
m^{2}=4a^{2}
is the result of re-summing an infinite series of genuine modular form contributions. Note that the logarithmic term produced by
{\cal F}_{0}
is required to obtain the
n=1
term needed to complete the series for the logarithm.

15.8.3

Modular properties

Exceptionally, in this subsection only, we shall parametrize
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations with Greek letters in order to avoid a clash with the notation for the periods
a
and
a_{D}
. Under modular modular transformations,

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}={\alpha\tau+\beta\over\gamma\tau+\delta}\hskip 43.36243pt\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{\omega}_{2}\\
\tilde{\omega}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\omega_{2}\\
\omega_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 43.36243pt\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{matrix}\right)\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})

(15.8.18)

we have the following transformations of Eisenstein series for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{2n}(\tilde{\tau})=(\gamma\tau+\delta)^{2n}{\sf E}_{2n}(\tau)+{12\over 2\pi i}\,\gamma(\gamma\tau+\delta)\,\delta_{n,1}

(15.8.19)

and the Weierstrass function, in which we keep the period
\omega_{1}=2\pi i
fixed,

\displaystyle\wp(\tilde{z}|\tilde{\tau})=(\gamma\tau+\delta)^{2}\wp(z|\tau)\hskip 72.26999pt\tilde{z}={z\over\gamma\tau+\delta}

(15.8.20)

The transformation laws of the SW data may be deduced as follows. First of all, modular invariance of the SW differential implies the transformation law for
k^{2}
,

\displaystyle\lambda=k\,dz=\tilde{k}\,d\tilde{z}\hskip 72.26999pt\tilde{k}^{2}=(\gamma\tau+\delta)^{2}k^{2}

(15.8.21)

Using the relation between
z,k,
and
\kappa
, we readily obtain,

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}=(\gamma\tau+\delta)\kappa

(15.8.22)

Here, the sign ambiguity introduced by taking the square root of
\kappa^{2}
amounts to implementing the transformation
-I\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on
\kappa
. The transformation laws on
a_{D},a
may be deduced from their explicit expressions in terms of
\kappa
and
\tau
. We begin by observing in (
15.8.11
) that the combination
a_{D}-\tau a
transforms as a modular form of weight
(-1,0)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{D}-\tilde{\tau}\tilde{a}=(\gamma\tau+\delta)^{-1}(a_{D}-\tau a)

(15.8.23)

Next, we use the explicit expression for
a
from (
15.8.10
) along with the transformation laws of
\kappa
,
K_{2n},
and
L_{2n-2}
as modular forms of weight
(1,0),(2n,0)
, and
(2n-2,0)
respectively, as well as the transformation law of
{\sf E}_{2}
, and we obtain,

\displaystyle\tilde{a}=(\gamma\tau+\delta)a+\gamma(a_{D}-\tau a)=\gamma\,a_{D}+\delta\,a

(15.8.24)

where the second term in the middle equality arises from the inhomogeneous transformation term of
{\sf E}_{2}
. Assembling both transformation laws we obtain,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{a}_{D}\\
\tilde{a}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}a_{D}\\
a\end{matrix}\right)

(15.8.25)

which is precisely the transformation law induced by the action of the modular group on the cycles
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
for a modular invariant SW differential.

15.9

Linear quiver chains from a limit of
{\cal N}=2^{*}

We now return to the
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory for the gauge group
SU(N)
, whose independent free parameters are the UV gauge coupling
\tau
, the hypermultiplet mass
m
, and the vacuum expectation values
a_{I}
of the gauge scalars, or equivalently the roots of the polynomial
P(k)
. The masses of the gauge boson BPS states are
|a_{I}-a_{J}|
while the masses of the BPS hypermultiplet states are
|a_{I}-a_{J}-m|
. Several interesting limits may be taken in this set-up.

A first interesting limit is obtained by keeping the vacuum expectation values
a_{I}
fixed while sending the hypermultiplet mass
m\to\infty
and letting
\tau\to i\infty
at the rate dictated by the one-loop renormalization group
\beta
-function with renormalization scale
\Lambda
(not to be confused with the lattice
\Lambda
),
\Lambda^{2N}=(-)^{N}m^{2N}q
where
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
. This gives the pure
{\cal N}=2
theory with gauge group
SU(N)
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
k
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
v_{4}
v_{5}
SU(N_{1})
SU(N_{2})
SU(N_{3})
SU(N_{4})
SU(N_{5})

Figure 18
:
The distribution of vacuum expectation values for a breaking from
SU(N)
to
SU(N_{1})\times\cdots\times SU(N_{5})
with
N=N_{1}+\cdots+N_{5}
where the
v_{p}
are separated from their nearest neighbors by
\pm m
plus a term
\Lambda_{p}
that is held fixed as
m,v_{p}\to\infty
. The green rectangles schematically represent the ranges in which the
x^{p}_{i_{p}}
can vary.

A second interesting limit is obtained by letting
m\to\infty
and
\tau\to\infty
, but now also sending some of the vacuum expectation values
a_{I}
to infinity. Upon carefully tuning the correlations between these limits, one can send some of the hypermultiplet masses and some of the gauge boson masses to
\infty
. This limit will break the gauge symmetry to
SU(N_{1})\times\cdots\times SU(N_{P})

with
N=N_{1}+\cdots+N_{P}
and
N_{p}\geq 2
for all
p=1,\cdots,P
, while any
U(1)
factors in the gauge group that arise may be shown to decouple. To obtain the above breaking pattern, we arrange the classical values for the expectation values in a linear chain. We partition all the order parameters
k_{I}
for
I=1,\cdots,N
into
P
groups with
I=i_{1}=1,\cdots,N_{1}
labeling the indices in the first group,
I=N_{1}+i_{2}
with
i_{2}=1,\cdots,N_{2}
those in the second group;
I=N_{1}+\cdots+N_{p-1}+i_{p}
with
i_{p}=1,\cdots,N_{p}
those in group
p
, and so on until
I=N_{1}+\cdots N_{P-1}+i_{P}
with
i_{P}=1,\cdots,N_{P}
. We now set,

\displaystyle k_{N_{1}+\cdots+N_{p}+i_{p}}=v_{p}+x^{p}_{i_{p}}\hskip 72.26999ptp=1,\cdots,P

(15.9.1)

The decomposition is not unique as it stands since
v_{p}
and all
x^{p}_{i_{p}}
may be shifted by an
i_{p}
-independent amount such that the corresponding
k_{I}
is unchanged. To make the decomposition unique, we impose the following further conditions,

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{P}N_{p}v_{p}=0\hskip 72.26999pt\sum_{i_{p}=1}^{N_{p}}x^{p}_{i_{p}}=0

(15.9.2)

We then order the values of
v_{p}
such that,

\displaystyle v_{p+1}-v_{p}=m+\Lambda_{p}

(15.9.3)

and then send
v_{p},m\to\infty
along with
\tau\to i\infty
while keeping all
\Lambda_{p}
and
x^{p}_{i_{p}}
fixed. The result is a linear quiver chain, depicted in Figure
18
. The gauge group is
SU(N_{1})\times\cdots\times SU(N_{P})
, and the theory contains hypermultiplets in bi-fundamental representations,

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{p=1}^{P-1}\Big\{({\bf N}_{p},\overline{{\bf N}}_{p+1})\oplus(\overline{{\bf N}}_{p},{\bf N}_{p+1})\Big\}

(15.9.4)

Depending on the values of
N_{p}
, hypermultiplets in the fundamental of
SU(N)
may also arise.

15.10

{\cal N}=2
dualities

In the remainder of this section we discuss a set of dualities between
{\cal N}=2
theories which makes interesting contact with the moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces. The theories in question will be superconformal, i.e. theories with both supersymmetry and conformal symmetry. We begin by briefly reviewing these notions.

15.10.1

Conformal symmetry

The conformal algebra in four dimensions is given by
\mathfrak{so}(4,2)
, generated by Lorentz transformations
L_{\mu\nu}
, translations
P_{\mu}
, dilations
D
, and special conformal transformations
K_{\mu}
. These generators satisfy the following commutation relations,

\displaystyle[L_{\mu\nu},L_{\rho\sigma}]

\displaystyle=\eta_{\nu\rho}L_{\mu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}L_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\rho}L_{\nu\sigma}-\eta_{\nu\sigma}L_{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle{[K_{\mu},P_{\nu}]}

\displaystyle=2(\eta_{\mu\nu}D-L_{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle{[L_{\mu\nu},P_{\rho}]}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\nu\rho}P_{\mu}-\eta_{\mu\rho}P_{\nu}

\displaystyle{[D,P_{\mu}]}

\displaystyle=P_{\mu}

\displaystyle{[L_{\mu\nu},K_{\rho}]}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\nu\rho}K_{\mu}-\eta_{\mu\rho}K_{\nu}

\displaystyle{[D,K_{\mu}]}

\displaystyle=-K_{\mu}

(15.10.1)

while
[P_{\mu},P_{\nu}]=[K_{\mu},K_{\nu}]=[D,L_{\mu\nu}]=0
. Dilations
D
generate an Abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(1,1)\subset\mathfrak{so}(4,2)
that commutes with the Lorentz subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
generated by
L_{\mu\nu}
. Therefore, we may choose the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(4,2)
to be generated by
D
and by the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
. The dilation generator can be used to assign weights to the other generators. The eigenvalue of
D
is customarily denoted by
\Delta
and referred to as the conformal weight. The final two commutation relations are the statement that the momenta
P_{\mu}
and special conformal generators
K_{\mu}
have conformal weights
1
and
-1
, respectively. Furthermore, they imply that
P_{\mu}
is not in the Cartan subalgebra, meaning that operators or states in a conformal field theory cannot be labelled by an energy/mass—indeed, such quantities would introduce a dimensionful scale to the theory and thereby violate conformal symmetry.

Conformal fields have already been introduced in section
5.1.1
, where they were labelled by weights
(h,\overline{h})
, related to the conformal weight
\Delta
via
\Delta=h+\overline{h}
. An important difference between the discussion here and in section
5.1.1
is that in the latter case the discussion was specific to two spacetime dimensions, for which the “global” conformal group discussed in this section is enhanced to (two copies of) an infinite-dimensional Virasoro symmetry, given in (
5.1.3
). In higher dimensions such an enhancement does not occur, though the notion of conformal primary introduced in section
5.1.1
persists. In the current language, a state
|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle
labelled by its conformal weight
\Delta
and representation of the Lorentz group
\mathfrak{so}(3,1)\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)
is a conformal primary if it satisfies,

\displaystyle K_{\mu}|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle=0

(15.10.2)

Starting from this highest weight state, a series of conformal descendants can be obtained via repeated application of
P_{\mu}
.

15.10.2

Superconformal symmetry

When
{\cal N}
supercharges transforming in the Weyl spinor representation of
\mathfrak{so}(3,1)
are added to the conformal algebra
\mathfrak{so}(4,2)
, one obtains the superalgebra
\mathfrak{su}(2,2|{\cal N})
. In the current section we will be mainly interested in the case of
{\cal N}=2
, for which the maximal bosonic subalgebra of the superconformal algebra is,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}(4,2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(2)_{R}\subset\mathfrak{su}(2,2|2)

(15.10.3)

The
\mathfrak{u}(2)_{R}
factor is referred to as the R-symmetry, and its generators will be denoted by
R^{I}_{J}
. They satisfy the standard commutation relations,

\displaystyle[R^{I}_{J},R^{K}_{L}]=\delta_{J}^{K}R^{I}_{L}-\delta^{I}_{L}R^{K}_{J}

(15.10.4)

As for the fermionic generators, in addition to the Poincaré supercharges
Q_{\alpha}^{I}
,
\overline{Q}_{{\dot{\alpha}}I}
, we now have the superconformal partners
S_{\alpha I}
,
\overline{S}_{{\dot{\alpha}}}^{I}
. The anti-commutation relations involving these generators take the form,

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},Q_{\beta}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{S_{\alpha I},S_{\beta J}\}=\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\overline{S}_{\dot{\beta}}^{I}\}=0

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\overline{Q}_{\dot{\beta}J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}P_{\mu}\delta^{I}_{J}

\displaystyle\{S_{\alpha I},\overline{S}_{\dot{\beta}}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}K_{\mu}\delta^{J}_{I}

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},S_{\beta J}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}(\delta_{J}^{I}\,D+R^{I}_{J})+{1\over 2}\delta^{I}_{J}\,L_{\mu\nu}\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}

(15.10.5)

Note that unlike in the non-conformal case, the superconformal algebra does not admit a deformation by central charges
Z
, since these would introduce a scale and thus would break conformal symmetry.

The states of an
{\cal N}=2
superconformal field theory can be labelled by their conformal weight
\Delta
and Lorentz quantum numbers
[j_{1},j_{2}]
, as well as additional labels
(R,r)
for the
\mathfrak{u}(2)_{R}\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)_{R}\times\mathfrak{u}(1)_{r}
R-symmetry. We will denote such states by
|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle^{(R,r)}
. In analogy to a conformal primary, one defines a superconformal primary via

\displaystyle S_{\alpha I}|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle^{(R,r)}\,\,=\,\,\overline{S}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle^{(R,r)}\,\,=\,\,0

(15.10.6)

Note that these two conditions, together with the anti-commutation relation in (
15.10.2
), imply that all superconformal primaries satisfy
K_{\mu}|[j_{1},j_{2}],\Delta\rangle^{(R,r)}=0
, and hence are particular cases of conformal primaries in the definition of (
15.10.2
). A superconformal multiplet is obtained by beginning with a superconformal primary and applying arbitrary combinations of
Q_{\alpha}^{I},\overline{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}I}
. By the anti-commutation relations in (
15.10.2
), this also includes all applications of the momentum operator
P_{\mu}
, and hence includes all conformal descendants.

15.10.3

Superconformal field theories

We may construct
{\cal N}=2
superconformal Lagrangians by combining the
{\cal N}=2
supermultiplets discussed in subsection
15.1.2
in such a way as to cancel any potential non-conformality. Setting all masses for the hypermultiplets to zero, the only parameter left in an
{\cal N}=2
Lagrangian is the coupling
\tau
, which is classically dimensionless. Thus any such Lagrangian would naively seem to have conformal invariance. While this is true at the classical level, quantum mechanically the coupling
\tau
—or rather only
g
, since
\theta
is topologically protected—can receive radiative corrections making it dimensionful. The dependence of the quantum-corrected coupling on the energy scale
\mu
is captured by the
\beta
-function,

\displaystyle{\beta(g)}=\mu{d\over d\mu}\left({8\pi^{2}\over g^{2}}\right)

(15.10.7)

which, for a generic four-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with
N_{f}
Weyl fermions in a representation
{\cal R}_{f}
and
N_{s}
scalars in a representation
{\cal R}_{s}
, is given by,

\displaystyle{\beta(g)}={11\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})-{2\over 3}N_{f}C({\cal R}_{f})-{1\over 3}N_{s}C({\cal R}_{s})+{\cal O}(g)

(15.10.8)

Here
C({\cal R})
is the Dynkin index of the representation
{\cal R}
, defined via
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\cal R}}(T^{a}T^{b})=C({\cal R})\delta^{ab}
with
T^{a}
the generators of the gauge algebra. By a slight abuse of notation, we shall denote the adjoint representation of the algebra
\mathfrak{g}
by the same symbol
\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,
C(\mathfrak{g})=h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number.

For an
{\cal N}=1
theory the vector multiplet, which contains one vector and one Weyl fermion in the adjoint representation, contributes a factor of,

\displaystyle{11\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})-{2\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})=3\,h^{\vee}

(15.10.9)

to the
\beta
-function. The contribution for a chiral multiplet, containing a Weyl fermion and a scalar both in the representation
{\cal R}
, is given by,

\displaystyle-{2\over 3}C({\cal R})-{1\over 3}C({\cal R})=-C({\cal R})

(15.10.10)

For example, an
{\cal N}=1
super-Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
SU(N_{c})
(for which
h^{\vee}=N_{c}
) with
N_{f}
chiral multiplets in the representation
{\cal R}
has the following
\beta
-function,

\displaystyle{\beta(g)}=3N_{c}-N_{f}C({\cal R})+{\cal O}(g)

(15.10.11)

For conformality, we see that we must require
N_{f}C({\cal R})=3N_{c}
.

We now move on to the main case of interest to us, namely
{\cal N}=2
theories. The contribution to the
\beta
-function of an
{\cal N}=2
vector multiplet is,

\displaystyle{11\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})-2\times{2\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})-{1\over 3}C(\mathfrak{g})=2\,h^{\vee}

(15.10.12)

while the contribution from a hypermultiplet in the representation
{\cal R}
is,

\displaystyle-2\times{2\over 3}C({\cal R})-2\times{1\over 3}C({\cal R})=-2\,C({\cal R})

(15.10.13)

We thus see that for an
{\cal N}=2

SU(N_{c})
gauge theory with
N_{f}
hypermultiplets in the representation
{\cal R}
,

\displaystyle{\beta(g)}=2N_{c}-2N_{f}C({\cal R})+{\cal O}(g)

(15.10.14)

Taking in particular the case in which all hypermultiplets are in the fundamental representation for which
C(\square)={1\over 2}
, we see that conformality can be achieved only if
N_{f}=2N_{c}
.

The theories that we will study in the rest of this section will be exactly of this type, namely
{\cal N}=2
theories with
SU(N_{c})
gauge group and
N_{f}
fundamental hypermultiplets. The data of such a theory can be captured in a so-called ‘‘quiver diagram”---a diagram whose internal nodes are gauge groups, whose external nodes are fundamental hypermultiplets, and whose internal edges are bifundamental hypermultiplets, with the structure of the quiver chosen to be compatible with the vanishing of all beta functions.
34
34

34

One could ask why we allow for only bifundamental edges in the graph. It turns out that for more complicated edges, it is impossible to satisfy both anomaly cancellation and vanishing of the beta functions. The only exception is the case of trifundamentals in theories with
SU(2)
gauge nodes, which we will return to below.
For example,
(N)-[2N]
represents
SU(N)
gauge theory with
2N
fundamentals, and has a manifestly vanishing beta function. Likewise the quiver

\displaystyle[N]-(N)-(N)-[N]

(15.10.15)

represents
SU(N)\times SU(N)
gauge theory with one bifundamental hypermultiplet corresponding to the internal edge, and with
N
additional fundamentals for each copy of
SU(N)
. Since the internal edge can be interpreted as giving
N
fundamental hypers to the left gauge node and
N
anti-fundamental hypers to the right gauge node, the beta functions for both gauge couplings indeed vanish.

We will focus on the case of
N=2
, for which something rather special happens. In particular, note that in this case each bifundamental has two
SU(2)
gauges indices and one
SU(2)
flavor index, i.e. we can write them as
\hat{Q}_{ijs}
with indices as defined above. We can imagine weakly gauging the flavor symmetry, or conversely ungauging one of the gauge symmetries, which makes it clear that all three indices should be treated democratically. In other words, it is useful to think of the elementary building blocks as trifundamental matter, which we will represent pictorially by

Conversely, any trivalent graphs can be given an interpretation as a SCFT with
SU(2)
nodes.

15.10.4

SU(2)
quivers and Riemann surfaces

Consider a trivalent graph
\Gamma
with
g
faces and
n
external legs. We will denote the theories obtained from such graphs by
{\cal T}_{g,n}
. There is one gauge
SU(2)
per internal edge, and it can be shown that the number of such internal edges is
3g-3+n
. To each of these internal nodes is associated a gauge coupling
\tau
. The quantity
3g-3+n
appearing here may seem familiar—indeed, if
n=0
then this precisely the dimension of the moduli space of genus
g
Riemann surfaces, as given in (
C.7.9
). For non-zero
n
, this gives the dimension of the moduli space of genus
g
Riemann surfaces with
n
marked points.

The mapping between the moduli space of 4d
{\cal N}=2
SU(2) quiver theories and that of punctured Riemann surfaces can be made more precise as follows. We identify each trivalent vertex with a three-punctured sphere,

Gauging involves gluing two such trivalent vertices together, which in other words corresponds to plumbing together two three-punctured spheres.

Indeed, let us consider gluing two trivalent vertices together to obtain the
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
theory. In the corresponding Riemann surface picture, we would like to plumb together two spheres along their punctures. Say that the two spheres have complex coordinates
w
and
z
, with the three punctures being located at
w,z=0,1,\infty
. We will glue the point at
w=\infty
to the point at
z=0
,

\times\,\,\,1
0\,\,\tiny\times
\tiny\times
\times\,\,\,1
\tiny\times
\tiny\times\,\,\infty

To glue them, we begin by changing to the local coordinate
\tilde{w}={1\over w}
which describes the region around the
w=\infty
puncture, and then we identify

\displaystyle z\tilde{w}=q

(15.10.16)

with
q
some complex parameter. This parameter is identified with the coupling in the field theory via
q=e^{i\pi\tau}
.

From this picture, it is clear that the conformal manifold of the
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
theory—that is to say the space in which
\tau
can take values—can be identified with the moduli space of a four-punctured sphere. The latter is none other than the usual fundamental domain, shown in Figure
6
. The cusp at
\tau=i\infty
, i.e.
q\rightarrow 0
, corresponds to the separating limit in which the four-punctured sphere pinches off into two three-punctured spheres.

It is also useful to consider the moduli space of a four-punctured sphere when the four punctures are taken to be distinguishable. In that case, the moduli space takes the form shown in Figure
19
. There are now three cusps, at
\tau=0,1,i\infty
, which correspond to configurations in which different pairs of marked points collide. It is interesting to consider the behavior of the field theory at each of these three cusps.

0
-{1\over 2}
{1\over 2}
-1
1

Figure 19
:
The fundamental domain for a sphere with four distinguishable marked points.

Let us begin with the weakly-coupled cusp at
\tau=i\infty
. At this point the theory is well-described by the usual
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
theory discussed before. This theory has an
SO(8)
flavor symmetry, under which the hypermultiplets transform in the
\mathbf{8}_{v}
representation. It will be useful to consider the following subgroup of
SO(8)
,

\displaystyle SO(8)\supset SO(4)\times SO(4)\supset SU(2)_{a}\times SU(2)_{b}\times SU(2)_{c}\times SU(2)_{d}

(15.10.17)

This is the subgroup which is manifest in the trivalent diagram. In particular, each of the four external legs carries one copy of
SU(2)
. Let us assign them as follows,

We see that in all cases, the quiver is of the same general form as before, suggesting that the strong-coupling regime of
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
is again described by
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
. However, distinguishing between the marked points allows us to make an important observation about these strong-coupling dualities. In particular, let us recall that in the original weakly-coupled theory, the hypermultiplets transformed in the
\mathbf{8}_{v}
of
SO(8)
. Decomposing this representation into those of the
SU(2)
subgroups, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{8}_{v}=\mathbf{4}\oplus\mathbf{4}=(\mathbf{2}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{b})\oplus(\mathbf{2}_{c}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{d})

(15.10.18)

On the other hand, one has the following decompositions,

\displaystyle\mathbf{8}_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbf{2}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{c})\oplus(\mathbf{2}_{b}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{d})

\displaystyle\mathbf{8}_{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbf{2}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{d})\oplus(\mathbf{2}_{b}\otimes\mathbf{2}_{c})

(15.10.19)

with
\mathbf{8}_{s}
and
\mathbf{8}_{c}
the spinor and conjugate spinor representations of
SO(8)
. Thus swapping
SU(2)_{b}
with
SU(2)_{c}
has the effect of switching from the vector to the spinor representation of
SO(8)
, and likewise for swapping
SU(2)_{b}
with
SU(2)_{d}
. We thus see that upon approaching the strongly-coupled cusps in moduli space, we reobtain
SU(2)

N_{f}=4
, but with the hypermultiplets transforming in spinor representations of the flavor symmetry group.

Similar statements hold for the
[2]-(2)-(2)-[2]
quiver whose trivalent diagram was given above. Again, approaching any cusp gives rise to a quiver in the same form, but with a rearrangement of the
SU(2)
subgroups of the total flavor symmetry. In general, though, the story is far richer. Indeed, it is often possible that the theory emerging at a cusp has a completely distinct quiver than that of the original theory. This for example happens in the case of the three node quiver
[2]-(2)-(2)-(2)-[2]
. In particular, if we consider the strong-coupling limit of the middle node, while keeping the outer nodes weakly coupled, the quiver transforms as
