which gives a quiver of an entirely new type.

We close this subsection by noting that a physical origin of the correspondence between 4d
{\cal N}=2
theories and Riemann surfaces can be obtained via M-theory. In particular, if we wrap an M5-brane on the relevant Riemann surfaces, with the insertion of appropriate defect operators at the marked points, then reducing the worldvolume theory from six- to four-dimension gives precisely the 4d
{\cal N}=2
theories in question. Due to their six-dimensional origins, the 4d
{\cal N}=2
theories constructed in this way are referred to as “Class
{\cal S}
” theories.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

Comprehensive expositions of supersymmetric quantum field theories and supersymmetric Yang Mills theories may be found in the books by Wess and Bagger
[
282
]
, Weinberg
[
284
]
, Freedman and Van Proeyen
[
285
]
, and Shifman
[
286
]
.

Goddard-Nuyts-Olive (GNO) duality was introduced in
[
287
]
and realized in
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theory as Montonen-Olive duality in
[
288
,
289
]
. The effect of topological charges on the central charge of the supersymmetry algebra was obtained in
[
290
]
, while the effect by which magnetic monopoles in the presence of a
\theta
-angle become dyons was derived in
[
291
]
. Subsequent tests of S-duality in
{\cal N}=4
were carried out in
[
42
]
. The relation between electric-magnetic duality and the geometric Langlands program was established in
[
292
]
. Integrated correlators in the superconformal phase of
{\cal N}=4
at fixed gauge coupling provided explicit realizations of S-duality
[
293
,
294
,
295
,
296
]
. For a SAGEX overview of modular invariance in
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills theory and Type IIB superstring theory, see
[
297
]
.

Seiberg-Witten theory was originally formulated for gauge group
SU(2)
in
[
149
,
150
]
. Generalizations to other gauge groups with various hypermultiplet contents were soon thereafter produced, and are reviewed in the lecture notes
[
298
,
299
,
300
,
301
]
and in the book by Tachikawa
[
302
]
, where extensive bibliographical references may also be found.

Construction of the prepotential using localization techniques was initiated for the instanton part in
[
303
]
, and generalized to include also the perturbative contributions in
[
304
]
. An overview of localization techniques is provided by the collection of essays in
[
305
]
.

The relation between integrable systems and the Seiberg-Witten solution for the
{\cal N}=2^{*}
theory was put forward in
[
306
,
307
]
. The implementation using the Calogero-Moser system for
SU(N)
gauge group was carried out in
[
308
]
, and for other gauge groups in
[
309
,
310
]
where the behavior under
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
transformations was also analyzed. The derivations in section
15.8
closely follow
[
308
,
311
]
, and reproduce the hypermultiplet mass expansion of
[
312
]
. The modular properties of the Seiberg-Witten solution for gauge group
SU(2)
and
N_{f}\leq 4
fundamentals were analyzed in detail in
[

,
314
]
and for
SU(3)
in
[
315
]
. The role of bi-modular forms was discussed in those references, in
[

]
, and in the mathematical literature in
[

]
. The Seiberg-Witten curve for linear quivers and gauge group
SU(N)
was constructed from M-theory in
[

]
.

The dualities of
{\cal N}=2
superconformal Yang-Mills theories were developed in
[

]
and are reviewed in
[
320
]
. The global structure and spectrum of line operators in
{\cal N}=4
Yang-Mills is clearly presented in
[
283
]
. Useful lecture notes on the dynamics of four-dimensional supersymmetric gauge theories may be found in
[

]
.

Besides the beautiful Mathematics which arose from Seiberg-Witten theory, various exciting physical results also emerged, including the discovery of a number of strongly-coupled, non-Lagrangian theories known as Argyres-Douglas theories
[
151
]
, as well as the later-discovered Minahan-Nemeschansky theories
[
152
,
153
]
. These bear close relation to complex-multiplication, as well as to Kodaira’s theory of the degeneration of elliptic fibers.

Finally, let us mention that in the last decade an interesting connection between four-dimensional
{\cal N}=2
theories and 2d vertex operator algebras (VOAs) has been uncovered
[
322
]
, which has given even more direct connections between 4d
{\cal N}=2
theories and modularity. Indeed, by studying the modular differential equations satisfied by the corresponding VOAs
[
323
,
324
]
, it has been possible to provide a partial classification for low-rank 4d
{\cal N}=2
theories. The full power of these techniques remains to be explored.

16

Basic Galois Theory

We close these notes with a brief introduction to Galois theory, largely following the discussions of
[
325
,
326
]
. After having introduced the basic Mathematical ideas, we will illustrate their use in Physics through examples from conformal field theory.

16.1

Fields

In Mathematics, a field
35
35

35

Not to be confused with classical or quantum fields in Physics, such as the electro-magnetic field.
is a set
F
equipped with two binary operations
F\times F\to F
on elements
a,b\in F
, referred to as addition
a+b
and multiplication
ab=a\cdot b
. Under addition
F
forms a group with unit element 0, while under multiplication
F\setminus\{0\}
forms a group with unit 1. The two operations are commutative and are intertwined by the property of distributivity which requires
a\cdot(b+c)=a\cdot b+a\cdot c
for all
a,b,c\in F
.

A field
F
is said to have characteristic 0 if there exists no integer
n
such that the
n
-fold sum of the unit 1 vanishes. If there does exist a positive integer
n
such that the
n
-fold sum of the unit 1 vanishes, then the smallest such integer may be shown to be a prime number
p
and the characteristic of
F
is defined to be
p
. If
F
has characteristic
p
then we have
p\cdot a=0
for all
a\in F
. The simplest finite fields of characteristic
p
prime are
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}={\mathbb{Z}}/(p{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Well-known examples of fields of characteristic 0 include the rational numbers
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the real numbers
{\mathbb{R}}
, the complex numbers
{\mathbb{C}}
, and the algebraic numbers (namely complex numbers that satisfy a polynomial equation with rational coefficients). Rational functions of a single variable
x
form fields of characteristic 0, namely
{\mathbb{Q}}(x)
,
{\mathbb{R}}(x)
, and
{\mathbb{C}}(x)
depending on whether the coefficients in the rational function are in
{\mathbb{Q}}
,
{\mathbb{R}}
, or
{\mathbb{C}}
respectively.

A subset
F\subset K
of a field
K
is referred to as a subfield if
F
is a field with respect to the binary operations of the field
K
. In the above examples, we have the subfield inclusions
{\mathbb{Q}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, and
{\mathbb{Q}}(x)\subset{\mathbb{R}}(x)\subset{\mathbb{C}}(x)
, as well as
{\mathbb{Q}}\subset{\mathbb{Q}}(x)
,
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}(x)
, and
{\mathbb{C}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}(x)
.

16.2

Field Extensions

A field
K
is said to be an extension of a field
F
if
F
is a subfield of
K
, namely
F\subset K
. The degree of the extension, denoted
[K:F]
, is the dimension of
K
viewed as a vector space over the field
F
. If
F
is a subfield of a field
K
which itself is a subfield of a field
L
, then the following relation holds between the degrees of extension,

\displaystyle F\subset K\subset L\hskip 72.26999pt[L:F]=[L:K][K:F]

(16.2.1)

Equivalently, these relations hold when
L
is an extension of
K
which in turn is an extension of
F
. For example,
{\mathbb{C}}
is a field extension of
{\mathbb{R}}
by the element
i
, and the degree of the extension is
[{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathbb{R}}]=2
since
\{1,i\}
is a basis of
{\mathbb{C}}
over
{\mathbb{R}}
. On the other hand,
{\mathbb{R}}
is a field extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
of infinite order.

An element
y\in K
is said to be algebraic over
F
if there exists a polynomial relation,

\displaystyle a_{n}y^{n}+a_{n-1}y^{n-1}+\dots+a_{0}=0\hskip 50.58878pta_{n},\dots,a_{0}\in F

(16.2.2)

with
a_{n}\not=0
for some
n\geq 1
. Given an extension
K
containing an element
y
that is algebraic over
F
, there exists a unique monic irreducible polynomial
f(x)\in F[x]
that has
y
as a root. This polynomial is known as the minimal polynomial of
y
over
F
. The uniqueness is a non-trivial result—it implies that once we have found any monic irreducible polynomial in
F[x]
with
y
as a root, it must be the minimal polynomial.

As an example, consider
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
and
F={\mathbb{Q}}
. The polynomial
f(x)=x^{2}-3
is monic and irreducible in
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
, and has
\sqrt{3}
as a root. It is thus the minimal polynomial of
\sqrt{3}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. This simple example illustrates an important fact: the minimal polynomial is dependent on the choice of base field
F
. Indeed, if we had chosen
F={\mathbb{R}}
, then
f(x)
would have been reducible, splitting to
f(x)=(x-\sqrt{3})(x+\sqrt{3})
. Thus the minimal polynomial of
\sqrt{3}
over
{\mathbb{R}}
is instead
\tilde{f}(x)=x-\sqrt{3}
.

16.2.1

Simple extensions

Let
K
be an extension of
F
and
y
be an element in
K
. We denote by
F(y)
the intersection of all subfields of
K
which contain both
F
and
y
. This intersection is a field, since the intersection of subfields of
K
is itself a field. Since
F(y)
is contained in all subfields containing
F
and
y
, it is by definition the smallest subfield containing
F
and
y
. We refer to such a minimal extension
F(y)
as a simple extension of
F
by
y
. In fact, any finite extension of a field
F
of characteristic 0 is a simple extension. For algebraic
y
, namely
y
satisfying
f(y)=0
for the minimal polynomial
f
of degree
n
with coefficients in
F
, one has the following result.

Theorem 16.1

If
y
is algebraic over
F
, and
K
is a field extension of
F
by the element
y
, then the simple extension
F(y)
does not depend on
K
, and satisfies

\displaystyle F(y)\cong F[x]/f(x)\hskip 57.81621pt[F(y):F]=n

(16.2.3)

Thus,
F(y)
is completely determined by
F[x]
and the minimal polynomial
f
of
y
.

An analogous statement holds for finitely generated extensions
F(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{N})
, as long as
K
is an algebraic extension of
F
, i.e. if every element in
K
is algebraic over
F
.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
16.1
is that if
y
and
w
have the same minimal polynomial
f(x)
in
F[x]
, then
F(y)
is isomorphic to
F(w)
.

16.2.2

Splitting fields

So far we have considered extension fields of
F
containing a root of a polynomial
f(x)\in F[x]
. We now consider extension fields that contain all the roots of
f(x)
. In particular, let
K
be an extension field of
F
and
f(x)
be a non-constant polynomial of degree
n
in
F[x]
. If
f(x)
factors in
K[x]
as
f(x)=\alpha(x-y_{1})(x-y_{2})\dots(x-y_{n})
, then we say that
f(x)
splits in
K
. If
K
is the smallest extension containing all the roots of
f(x)
, then we refer to
K
as the splitting field for
f(x)
.

By Theorem
16.1
, any two splitting fields of a polynomial in
F[x]
are isomorphic. Furthermore, every polynomial
f(x)\in F[x]
admits a splitting field over
F
.

Theorem 16.2

Let
F
be a field and
f(x)
be a non-constant polynomial of degree
n
in
F(x)
. Then there exists a splitting field
K
of
f(x)
over
F
such that
[K:F]\leq n!
.

Of course, we already know that, if
K
is a simple extension and the polynomial
f(x)
has
n
distinct roots, then
[K:F]=n
by Theorem
16.1
.

We have just noted that every polynomial admits a splitting field; it is then natural to ask if there exists a field extension of
F
over which every polynomial splits. If such a universal splitting field exists, the field
F
is said to be algebraically closed. A well-known example is
F={\mathbb{C}}
. Another is the field
{\mathbb{A}}
of all algebraic numbers over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

16.2.3

Normal extensions

We close this subsection by introducing the concept of a normal extension. An algebraic extension
K
of
F
is said to be normal provided that whenever an irreducible polynomial has one root in
K
, it has all roots in
K
(i.e. it splits over
K
). For example,
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
is a normal extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
since both of the roots
\pm\sqrt{3}
of the minimal polynomial
x^{2}-3
are in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
. On the other hand, the extension
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2})
is not a normal extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
since two roots of the minimal polynomial
x^{3}-2
are complex and hence not contained in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2})
.

16.3

Field automorphisms and the Galois Group

An automorphism of a field
K
is a bijection
\sigma:K\rightarrow K
such that for all
a,b\in K
we have,

\displaystyle\sigma(a+b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)

\displaystyle\sigma(ab)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(a)\,\sigma(b)

(16.3.1)

Two automorphisms
\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}
are distinct if
\sigma(a)\neq\tilde{\sigma}(a)
for at least one element
a\in K
. Distinct automorphisms are linearly independent—that is, if
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
are distinct automorphisms of
K
, then
a linear relation valid for all
y\in K
,

\displaystyle a_{1}\sigma_{1}(y)+\dots+a_{n}\sigma_{n}(y)=0

(16.3.2)

implies that
a_{1}=\dots=a_{n}=0
.

Let
K
be an extension field of
F
. An
F
-automorphism of
K
is an automorphism of the field
K
, namely
\sigma:K\rightarrow K
, that fixes each element of
F
. The set of all
F
-automorphisms will be denoted
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
and is called the Galois group of
K
over
F
. The use of the word “group” is justified since
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
can be endowed with group structure via function composition. Indeed, if
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
then clearly
\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}
is also an automorphism of
K
leavings
F
invariant. We begin with the following simple result,

Theorem 16.3

Consider a polynomial
f(x)\in F[x]
. If
y\in K
is a root of
f(x)
and
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
, then
\sigma(y)
is also a root of
f(x)
.

To prove this theorem, let
f(x)
be an arbitrary polynomial of arbitrary degree
n
,

\displaystyle f(x)=c_{n}x^{n}+\dots+c_{1}x+c_{0}

(16.3.3)

Since
f(y)=0
, and
\sigma
is a homomorphism with
\sigma(c_{i})=c_{i}
for each
c_{i}\in F
, we have,

\displaystyle 0=\sigma(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma\left(c_{n}y^{n}+\dots c_{1}y+c_{0}\right)

(16.3.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(c_{n})\sigma(y^{n})+\dots+\sigma(c_{1})\sigma(y)+\sigma(c_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n}\sigma(y)^{n}+\dots+c_{1}\sigma(y)+c_{0}

and hence
\sigma(y)
is a root of
f(x)
.

Theorem
16.3
shows that every image of a root
y
of
f(x)
under an automorphism of the Galois group is also a root of
f(x)
. Conversely, we could ask if every root of
f(x)
can be obtained as the image of some root
y
under an automorphism
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
. In some instances, this is indeed the case:

Theorem 16.4

If
K
is a splitting field for a polynomial in
F[x]
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in K
are two roots, then there exists an element
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
such that
\sigma(y_{1})=y_{2}
if and only if
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
have the same minimal polynomial over
F
.

The forward direction follows immediately from Theorem
16.3
. For the converse, note from Theorem
16.1
that if
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
have the same minimal polynomial, then there is an isomorphism
\sigma:F(y_{1})\cong F(y_{2})
. This isomorphism can be chosen such that
\sigma(y_{1})=y_{2}
, while fixing
F
elementwise. Since
K
is a splitting field of some polynomial in
F[x]
, it is also a splitting field for the same polynomial over
F(y_{1})
and
F(y_{2})
, and thus
\sigma
extends to an
F
-automorphism of
K
, i.e.
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
.

A important fact of which we make repeated use is the following,

Theorem 16.5

If
K=F(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
is an algebraic extension of
F
, then automorphisms in
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
are determined completely by their action on
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
. That is, if
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\rm Gal}(K,F)
satisfy
\sigma_{1}(y_{i})=\sigma_{2}(y_{i})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
, then
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
.

Together with Theorem
16.3
, this result can be used to at least partially construct concrete Galois groups. For example, consider the Galois group of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5})
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
By Theorem
16.5
the Galois automorphisms are determined completely by their action on
\sqrt{3}
and
\sqrt{5}
. The minimal polynomial of
\sqrt{3}
is
x^{2}-3
, and hence by Theorem
16.4
we must have
\sqrt{3}\rightarrow\pm\sqrt{3}
. Similar statements hold for
\sqrt{5}
. Hence the Galois group
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})
has at most four elements,

\displaystyle\sqrt{3}\xrightarrow{1}\sqrt{3}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{3}\xrightarrow{\alpha}-\sqrt{3}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{3}\xrightarrow{\beta}\sqrt{3}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{3}\xrightarrow{\gamma}-\sqrt{3}

\displaystyle\sqrt{5}\xrightarrow{}\sqrt{5}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{5}\xrightarrow{}\sqrt{5}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{5}\xrightarrow{}-\sqrt{5}\hskip 36.135pt\sqrt{5}\xrightarrow{}-\sqrt{5}

(16.3.5)

In fact, it turns out that in this case there are exactly four elements, and
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, though we will not prove this here.

In the above example, every automorphism of the Galois group permuted the four roots
\pm\sqrt{3},\pm\sqrt{5}
. This is a particular case of a more general result,

Theorem 16.6

If
K
is the splitting field of a separable polynomial
f(x)
of degree
n
in
F[x]
, then
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
S_{n}
.

That is, every element of
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
produces a permutation of the roots of
f(x)
. Of course, the converse is not necessarily true. For example, in the case of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5})
, the permutation mapping
\pm\sqrt{3}\rightarrow\pm\sqrt{5},\pm\sqrt{5}\rightarrow\pm\sqrt{3}
does not correspond to an
F
-automorphism of
K
.

16.3.1

Intermediate Fields

Let
K
be an extension field of
F
. A field
E
such that
F\subseteq E\subseteq K
is called an intermediate field of the extension. It can be shown that the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(E,F)
associated to the intermediate field is a subgroup of
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
. Hence to every intermediate field we can associate a subgroup of the Galois group. Conversely, if
H
is a subgroup of the Galois group, one can associate to it an intermediate field
E_{H}
such that,

\displaystyle E_{H}=\left\{k\in K\,|\,\sigma(k)=k\quad\forall\,\sigma\in H\right\}

(16.3.6)

known as the fixed field of
H
. That
E_{H}
is indeed a field is easy to check, since for example the equalities
\sigma(0)=0
and
\sigma(1)=1
imply that
0
and
1
are contained in
E_{H}
, and since,

\displaystyle\sigma(c+d)=\sigma(c)+\sigma(d)=c+d\hskip 36.135pt\sigma(cd)=\sigma(c)\sigma(d)=cd

(16.3.7)

imply that
E_{H}
is closed under addition and multiplication. The presence of inverses follow simply as well.
A somewhat stronger result is the following,

Theorem 16.7

Let
K
be a finite-dimensional extension field of
F
. If
H
is a subgroup of
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
and
E_{H}
is the fixed field of
H
, then
H={\rm Gal}(K,E_{H})
and
|H|=[K:E]
.

We may now return to the example of
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})
. In this case
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
is an intermediate field of the extension
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5})
of
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Recall that
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})=\{1,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
as defined in (
16.5
). On the other hand,
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3}))
should consist of only the transformations leaving the base field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
invariant. Hence we have that

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3}))=\{1,\beta\}

(16.3.8)

which is clearly a subgroup of
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})
. Conversely, the fixed field of
H=\{1,\beta\}
is precisely
E_{H}={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
.

16.4

The Fundamental Theorem of Galois Theory

Consider a finite-dimensional extension field
K
of
F
. Let
S
be the set of all intermediate fields, and
T
be the set of all subgroups of the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
. We introduce a function
\varphi:S\rightarrow T
such that for each intermediate field
E
,

\displaystyle\varphi(E)={\rm Gal}(K,E)

(16.4.1)

The function
\varphi
is referred to as a Galois correspondence. In this correspondence
K
is mapped to the identity subgroup of
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
, while
F
is mapped to the entire group
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
. By Theorem
16.7
, the Galois correspondence is surjective.

While surjectivity of the Galois correspondence is guaranteed, injectivity is not. For example, consider the extension field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2})
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Every automorphism in the Galois group maps
\sqrt[3]{2}
to a root of the minimal polynomial
x^{3}-2
by Theorem
16.3
. Since
\sqrt[3]{2}
is the only real root, and since all elements of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2})
are real, every automorphism must map
\sqrt[3]{2}
to itself. Thus by Theorem
16.5
we conclude that
{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2}),{\mathbb{Q}})
is trivial. This means that under the Galois correspondence, both
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt[3]{2})
and
{\mathbb{Q}}
must be associated to the same group, i.e. the trivial group
\{1\}
. Therefore the Galois correspondence is not injective.

In the cases in which the Galois correspondence is injective, the extension
K
of
F
is called a Galois extension. Given a Galois extension
K
, if
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are intermediate fields such that
{\rm Gal}(K,E_{1})={\rm Gal}(K,E_{2})
, then we can conclude that
E_{1}=E_{2}
. Note that in characteristic 0, a Galois extension is simply a splitting field.

As an example, consider the extension
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5})
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. This extension is Galois because it is the splitting field of
f(x)=(x^{2}-3)(x^{2}-5)
. In this case the Galois correspondence
\varphi
must be bijective, and indeed we have

\displaystyle\varphi:{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}))=1

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3}))=\{1,\beta\}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5}))=\{1,\alpha\}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{15})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5}))=\{1,\gamma\}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3},\sqrt{5}),{\mathbb{Q}})=\{1,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}

(16.4.2)

In this example we observe that all intermediate fields appearing are themselves Galois extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}
. For example,
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
is a Galois extension since it is the splitting field of
x^{2}-3
. Furthermore, the corresponding subgroups of the Galois group are all normal. The general version of this statement is given by the Fundamental Theorem of Galois Theory,

Theorem 16.8

Let
K
be a Galois extension of
F
. Then

1.

The map
\varphi:S\rightarrow T
between the set
S
of intermediate fields and the set
T
of subgroups of the Galois group
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
defined by
\varphi(E)={\rm Gal}(K,E)
is a bijection. One has

\displaystyle[K:E]=|{\rm Gal}(K,E)|\hskip 57.81621pt[E:F]=[{\rm Gal}(K,F):{\rm Gal}(K,E)]

(16.4.3)

2.

An intermediate field
E
is a normal extension of
F
if and only if the corresponding group
{\rm Gal}(K,E)
is a normal subgroup of
{\rm Gal}(K,F)
, in which case
{\rm Gal}(E,F)\cong{\rm Gal}(K,F)/{\rm Gal}(K,E)
.

16.5

Solvability by Radicals

The most famous application of Galois theory, and the reason for its initial development, is to the study of roots of polynomial equations. In particular, Galois theory provides a criterion for a polynomial to be solvable by radicals, as we now discuss. Let us first define the notion of solvability by radicals. A field
K
is said to be a radical extension of a field
F
if there exists a chain of fields,

\displaystyle F=F_{0}\subseteq F_{1}\subseteq F_{1}\subseteq F_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq F_{r}=K

(16.5.1)

such that for each
i=1,2,\dots,r
we have
F_{i}=F_{i-1}(y_{i})
and some power of
y_{i}
is in
F_{i-1}
.
Now let
f(x)\in F[x]
. The equation
f(x)=0
is said to be solvable by radicals if there exists a radical extension of
F
that contains a splitting field of
f(x)
.

On the other hand, a group
G
is said to be solvable if there exists a chain of subgroups,

\displaystyle G=G_{0}\supseteq G_{1}\supseteq G_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq G_{n-1}\supseteq G_{n}=\{1\}

(16.5.2)

such that
G_{i}
is a normal subgroup of the preceding group
G_{i-1}
and the quotient group
G_{i-1}/G_{i}
is abelian. For example, every Abelian group
G
is solvable since
G\supseteq\{1\}
is a chain of the required form.
As a more non-trivial example, consider the cyclic subgroup
\langle(123)\rangle
of order
3
in
S_{3}
. The chain,

\displaystyle S_{3}\supseteq\langle(123)\rangle\supseteq\langle(1)\rangle

(16.5.3)

shows that
S_{3}
is solvable. On the other hand, it can be shown that for
n\geq 5
the group
S_{n}
is not solvable.

We now state the famous Galois Criterion for solvability by radicals.

Theorem 16.9

Let
F
be a field of characteristic 0 and
f(x)\in F[x]
. Then
f(x)=0
is solvable by radicals if and only if the Galois group of
f(x)
is solvable.

As a simple example, consider the polynomial
f(x)=2x^{5}-10x+5\in{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. By Theorem
16.6
, the Galois group must be a subgroup of
S_{5}
. We will now show that it is in fact equal to
S_{5}
. To do so, note that the function
f(x)
has a maximum at
x=-1
, a minimum at
x=1
, and an inflection point at
x=0
. This means that
f(x)
crosses the
x
-axis exactly three times, and hence that
f(x)
has exactly three real roots. If
K
is a splitting field of
f(x)
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, then
|{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})|=[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]
by Theorem
16.8
. Taking any root
r
of
f(x)
, we have

\displaystyle[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=[K:{\mathbb{Q}}(r)][{\mathbb{Q}}(r):{\mathbb{Q}}]=5[K:{\mathbb{Q}}(r)]

(16.5.4)

by Theorem
16.1
. From this it follows that
5
divides
|{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})|
, and thus that
{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
contains an element of order 5. In other words,
{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
contains a 5-cycle of
S_{5}
. In addition,
{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
also contains complex conjugation, which fixes the three real roots of
f(x)
but interchanges the two non-real roots. Thus
{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
contains a transposition. But in fact, the only subgroup of
S_{5}
containing both a 5-cycle and a transposition is
S_{5}
itself. We thus conclude that
{\rm Gal}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})=S_{5}
. Then using the fact that
S_{n}
is not solvable for
n\geq 5
, we conclude from Theorem
16.9
that
f(x)
does not admit a solution in radicals. This implies the famous result that there is no universal formula (involving only field operations and roots) for the solutions of fifth-degree polynomial equations.

16.6

Galois Theory in rational conformal field theory

We close with an application of Galois theory to Physics. As discussed in sections
8.3.2
and
10.10
, the torus partition of a rational conformal field theory (RCFT) can be expressed in terms of a finite number of characters
\boldsymbol{\chi}(\tau)=(\chi_{1}(\tau),\dots,\chi_{d}(\tau))
, transforming in a vector representation of the modular group. The action of
S:\tau\rightarrow-1/\tau
in particular is implemented by a matrix known as the modular
S
-matrix,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}(-1/\tau)=S\,\boldsymbol{\chi}(\tau)

(16.6.1)

which, by slight abuse of notation, will also be denoted by
S
. A crucial constraint on
S
is that it satisfy the Verlinde formula,

\displaystyle N_{pq}^{r}=\sum_{s\in{\cal I}}{S_{p,s}S_{q,s}S^{\dagger}_{r,s}\over S_{0,s}}

(16.6.2)

where
s\in{\cal I}
labels the finite set of primary fields
\phi_{s}
of the theory and
N_{pq}^{r}
are fusion coefficients of primaries, namely they satisfy,

\displaystyle\phi_{p}\times\phi_{q}=\sum_{r}N_{pq}^{r}\phi_{r}

(16.6.3)

In particular, the operator product coefficients
N_{pq}^{r}
are integers. Treating
N_{p}
with entries
(N_{p})_{q}^{r}=N^{r}_{pq}
as matrices, we may write their characteristic polynomials as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left(\lambda_{p}\mathds{1}-N_{p}\right)=0

(16.6.4)

The polynomial in each
\lambda_{p}
is a monic with integer coefficients. From the Verlinde formula, it follows that the roots are given by,

\displaystyle\lambda_{p}^{(q)}={S_{p,q}\over S_{0,q}}

(16.6.5)

Whereas the matrix entries of
N_{p}
are integers, the ratios of S-matrix elements
\lambda_{p}^{(q)}
are generically not even rational. Consider the extension field
{\mathbb{Q}}(S_{p,q})
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, where
{\mathbb{Q}}(S_{p,q})
denotes extension by the matrix elements of the modular S-matrix. By Theorem
16.3
, the elements
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(S_{p,q}),{\mathbb{Q}})
act on the
\lambda_{p}^{(q)}
by permuting them amongst themselves. In particular, we have,

\displaystyle\sigma\left({S_{p,q}\over S_{0,q}}\right)={\sigma(S_{p,q})\over\sigma(S_{0,q})}={S_{p,\sigma(q)}\over S_{0,\sigma(q)}}

(16.6.6)

Note that here we have associated the permutation of the roots
\lambda_{p}^{(q)}
to a permutation of the underlying primary fields. Indeed, it is possible to prove that there exist a set of signs
\epsilon_{\sigma}(q)\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

\displaystyle\sigma(S_{p,q})=\epsilon_{\sigma}(q)S_{p,\sigma(q)}

(16.6.7)

i.e. Galois conjugation acts on the S-matrix by signed permutation.

This so-called Galois symmetry of the RCFT leads to the following physical constraints. Recall that the partition function of a RCFT is obtained by gluing together the chiral and anti-chiral characters via,

\displaystyle Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{(p,\bar{p})\,\in\,{\cal I}\,\times\,\overline{{\cal I}}}{\cal N}_{p,\bar{p}}\,\chi_{p}(\tau)\,\overline{\chi}_{\bar{p}}(\bar{\tau})

(16.6.8)

By modular invariance the gluing matrix
{\cal N}_{p,\bar{p}}
is required to satisfy
({\cal N}\overline{S})_{p,\bar{q}}=(S{\cal N})_{p,\bar{q}}
. We now apply a Galois automorphism
\sigma
to this equation, giving,

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{m}}{\cal N}_{p,\bar{m}}\bar{\epsilon}_{\sigma}(\bar{m})\overline{S}_{\sigma(\bar{m}),\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\sigma}(p)\sum_{m}S_{\sigma(p),m}{\cal N}_{m,\bar{q}}

(16.6.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\sigma}(p)\sum_{\bar{m}}{\cal N}_{\sigma(p),\bar{m}}\overline{S}_{\bar{m},\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\sigma}(p)\sum_{\bar{m}}{\cal N}_{\sigma(p),\sigma(\bar{m})}\overline{S}_{\sigma(\bar{m}),\bar{q}}

Using invertibility of
S
, we then obtain,

\displaystyle{\cal N}_{\sigma(p),\sigma(q)}=\epsilon_{\sigma}(p)\bar{\epsilon}_{\sigma}(\bar{q}){\cal N}_{p,\bar{q}}

(16.6.10)

Because
{\cal N}\geq 0
, we conclude that
{\cal N}_{p,\bar{q}}
can only be non-zero when
\epsilon_{\sigma}(p)=\bar{\epsilon}_{\sigma}(\bar{q})
for all
\sigma
. This turns out to be a very useful result, leading to powerful selection rules for possible modular invariant combinations of characters. In the literature on rational conformal field theory, it is often referred to as the “parity rule”.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

An all-time classic introduction to Galois theory may be found in the book by Artin
[
327
]
, while a general treatment of modern algebra pertinent to Galois theory is given in the book by van der Waerden
[
328
]
. The books by Escofier
[
325
]
and Cox
[
329
]
provide clear and systematic introductions with many examples, problems sets, and historical notes. We also refer to the recent book by Hungerford
[
326
]
.

In the context of rational conformal field theory, Galois symmetry first appeared in
[
330
]
, with numerous follow-up works including
[
331
,
332
,
333
]
. The connection to Hecke operators was discussed in
[
133
,
134
]
. Galois symmetry also plays a role in the study of three-dimensional topological quantum field theories, as discussed in e.g.
[
334
,
335
,
336
]
.

Part III

Appendix

Four relatively short appendices collect basic mathematical material that, while central to various parts of the core material in these lecture notes, is not concerned directly with either modular forms or string theory.

Appendix
A
is primarily included for the benefit of the reader with a physics training, and is presumably familiar to every reader with a mathematics training. It includes a basic review of arithmetic modulo
N
, the Chinese remainder theorem, solving polynomial equations in modular arithmetic, quadratic residue calculations, and the quadratic reciprocity theorem. We conclude with a brief introduction to Dirichlet characters and
L
-functions.

Appendix
B
provides a review of some of the basic definitions, theorems, and general results on compact Riemann surfaces needed in the main text. This material is directly relevant to the physical applications to conformal field theory discussed in section
5
; the structure of the modular curves in section
6
; the perturbative string amplitudes in section
12
; the topic of Seiberg-Witten theory in section
15
; and to the subsequent appendices
C
and
D
. The topological, metrical, and complex structural aspects of Riemann surfaces are reviewed as are their uniformization and construction in terms of Fuchsian groups.

Appendix
C
provides a review of holomorphic line bundles on compact Riemann surfaces, the Riemann-Roch theorem, the vanishing theorems, and their relation to the dimension of moduli space and to the construction of the spaces of tensors and spinor fields on Riemann surfaces. This material is directly relevant to the physical applications discussed in section
5
, to perturbative string amplitudes in section
12
, and to the material in appendix
D
.

Appendix
D
provides an overview of higher rank Riemann
\vartheta
-functions, the Riemann relations on
\vartheta
-functions, modular geometry of the Siegel upper half space, the Abel map, Jacobian varieties, and their relation to higher genus Riemann surfaces via the Riemann vanishing theorem. Using these ingredients, the construction of holomorphic and meromorphic differentials of arbitrary weight on Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus is carried out, using the prime form as an essential building block. As a Physics application, the correlation functions for the
bc
system, which were already discussed in the special case of genus one Riemann surfaces in section
5
, are evaluated explicitly using the properties of meromorphic differentials and the operator product expansion.

Appendix A

Some arithmetic

In this appendix we collect some basic results in number theory, including the Chinese remainder theorem, its application to solving polynomial equations, the Legendre and Jacobi symbols, quadratic reciprocity, its application to solving quadratic equations mod
N
, and a brief introduction to Dirichlet characters and
L
-functions. Throughout the set of positive integers is denoted
{\mathbb{N}}=\{1,2,3,\cdots\}
and does not include 0.

A.1

Arithmetic mod N

We denote by
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}={\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}}=\{0,1,2,\cdots,N-1\}
the additive group of integers modulo
N
. Addition of two elements
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is defined by
a+b~({\rm mod~}N)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
, the identity is
0
, and the inverse of
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is
-a~({\rm mod~}N)
. The group
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
is isomorphic to the multiplicative group of
N
-th roots of unity
\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{2},\cdots,\varepsilon^{N-1}\}
where
\varepsilon=e^{2\pi i/N}
and multiplication is in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

For
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
a prime number, the set
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{*}=\{1,2,\cdots,p-1\}
forms a group under multiplication of integers
({\rm mod~}p)
, and
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}=\{0,1,2,\cdots,p-1\}
forms a finite field.

For arbitrary
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}_{N}
the maximal multiplicative subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
. When
N
is prime, the definition coincides with the one provided above. When
N
is not prime, however, there are elements in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
other than 0 which are not invertible, and have to be removed to produce a multiplicative group. Consider an element
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
which is not relatively prime with
N
, namely such that
\gcd(a,N)>1
, then it follows that the element
b=N/\gcd(a,N)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
satisfies
ab=N
so that
ab\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
and therefore the element
a
is not invertible in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
. Every invertible element
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
must be relatively prime to
N
, so that the multiplicative subgroup is given by,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}=\{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N},~\gcd(a,N)=1\}\hskip 72.26999pt|{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}^{*}|=\phi(N)

(A.1.1)

where
\phi(N)
is Euler’s totient function counting precisely the number of integers less than
N
that are relatively prime to
N
, and
|S|
denotes the cardinality of the set
S
. For example, we may represent the group
{\mathbb{Z}}_{15}^{*}
by listing its elements in pairs of opposites modulo 15 as follows
{\mathbb{Z}}_{15}^{*}=\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 4,\pm 7\}
. The elements
\pm 1
and
\pm 4
square to 1
({\rm mod~}15)
, while
7\times(-2)\equiv(-7)\times 2\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}15)
.

A.2

Chinese remainder theorem

Theorem A.1

Let
n_{1},\cdots,n_{r}
be positive integers which are relatively prime in pairs so that they satisfy
\gcd(n_{i},n_{j})=1
for all
1\leq i\not=j\leq r
and let
b_{1},\cdots b_{r}
be arbitrary integers. Then the system of congruences,

\displaystyle x\equiv b_{i}~({\rm mod~}n_{i})\qquad\hbox{ for all }~i=1,\cdots,r

(A.2.1)

has a unique solution modulo
N=n_{1}\cdots n_{r}
. An equivalent statement is that the map,

\displaystyle x~({\rm mod~}N)\,\mapsto\,\big(x~({\rm mod~}n_{1}),\cdots,x~({\rm mod~}n_{r})\big)

(A.2.2)

is a ring isomorphism,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\,\cong\,{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{r}}

(A.2.3)

To prove the theorem, we begin by proving that the solution is unique, so that the map is injective. If we had two solutions
x,y
satisfying the same congruence conditions then
x-y\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}n_{i})
for all
i
. Since the
n_{i}
are pairwise co-prime their product
N
also divides
x-y
so that
x-y\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
. Since the cardinality of the left and right sides are equal to one another, the map must be surjective as well, and is thereby an isomorphism.

A.3

Solving polynomial equations

Theorem A.2

Let
f(x)
be a polynomial in
x
with integer coefficients
f\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
, let
n_{1},\cdots n_{r}
be positive integers which are relatively prime in pairs
\gcd(n_{i},n_{j})=1
for all
1\leq i\not=j\leq r
, and let
N=n_{1}\cdots n_{r}
. Then the congruence,

\displaystyle f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)

(A.3.1)

has a solution if and only if each one of the congruences,

\displaystyle f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}n_{i})\qquad i=1,\cdots,r

(A.3.2)

has a solution. The number of solutions
\nu(N)
to (
A.3.1
) is given in terms of the number of solutions
\nu(n_{i})
for each of the congruences by,

\displaystyle\nu(N)=\nu(n_{1})\cdots\nu(n_{r})

(A.3.3)

To prove the theorem we prove both implications. Clearly if
x
satisfies (
A.3.1
) then it satisfies (
A.3.2
). Conversely, let
x_{i}~({\rm mod~}n_{i})
be a solution to each congruence (
A.3.2
), which exists by the assumption of the theorem, then there exists
x~({\rm mod~}N)
which solves,

\displaystyle x\equiv x_{i}~({\rm mod~}n_{i})

(A.3.4)

by the Chinese remainder theorem.

Applying Theorem
A.2
to polynomial equations modulo an integer
N
whose prime factorization is given by
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
for
p_{i}
all distinct primes and
\alpha_{i}\geq 1
, we have the equivalence,

\displaystyle f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\big\{f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})\hbox{ for all }i\big\}

(A.3.5)

The individual congruences for each prime power are given as follows.

Theorem A.3

Assume
p
prime,
\alpha\geq 2
, and let
r
be a solution of the congruence,

\displaystyle f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})

(A.3.6)

with
0\leq r<p^{\alpha-1}
. The existence of solutions proceeds as follows.

(a) If
f^{\prime}(r)\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
then
r
lifts in a unique way from
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})
to
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
.

(b) If
f^{\prime}(r)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)

•

f(r)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
then
r
lifts from
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})
to
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
in
p
different ways.

•

f(r)\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
, then
r
cannot be lifted from
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})
to
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
.

To prove the theorem, we consider all possible lifts of
r
from
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})
to
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
, which are given by
r+qp^{\alpha-1}~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
. Taylor expanding the polynomial
f
of degree
N
,

\displaystyle f(r+qp^{\alpha-1})=f(r)+f^{\prime}(r)qp^{\alpha-1}+{1\over 2}f^{\prime\prime}(r)q^{2}p^{2\alpha-2}+\cdots+{1\over N!}f^{(N)}(r)q^{N}p^{N\alpha-N}

(A.3.7)

and reducing this equation
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
we obtain,

\displaystyle f(r+qp^{\alpha-1})\equiv f(r)+f^{\prime}(r)qp^{\alpha-1}~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})

(A.3.8)

Since
f(r)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha-1})
, we must have
f(r)=kp^{\alpha-1}
for some integer
k
. Therefore the number of solutions to
f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
equals the number of solutions to the congruence

k+qf^{\prime}(r)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
. The statements of the Theorem now readily follows.

A.4

Quadratic residues and quadratic residue symbols

If
m,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\gcd(n,N)=1
, then
n
is an
m
-th power residue mod
N
if
x^{m}\equiv n~({\rm mod~}N)
admits a solution, and a non-residue if it admits no solutions. In the special case of quadratic residues for
m=2
with prime
N=p
an odd prime, an integer
n\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
is a quadratic residue if the equation,

\displaystyle x^{2}\equiv n~({\rm mod~}p)

(A.4.1)

has a solution, and a quadratic non-residue if the equation has no solutions. If
n\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
is a quadratic residue and
x
is a solution to (
A.4.1
), then
-x
is also a solution to the same equation.
When
n\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
then equation (
A.4.1
) has one solution, namely
x\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
.

The Legendre symbol
(n|p)
for
p
prime combines all these cases and is defined as follows,
36
36

36

Frequently used alternative notations for the Legendre symbol are
({n\over p})
and
(n/p)
.

\displaystyle(n|p)=\left\{\begin{matrix}+1&\hbox{ if }n\hbox{ is a quadratic residue }{\rm mod~}p\cr-1&\hbox{ if }n\hbox{ is a quadratic non-residue }{\rm mod~}p\cr 0&\hbox{ if }n\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(A.4.2)

The number of solutions
x
to equation (
A.4.1
) is
1+(n|p)
in all cases.

Proposition A.4

Further properties of quadratic residues and the Legendre symbol for an arbitrary odd prime
p
are as follows.

1.

There are
(p-1)/2
residues and
(p-1)/2
non-residues in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{*}
;

2.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have
(n|p)=n^{(p-1)/2}~({\rm mod~}p)
;

3.

The first supplement is
(-1|p)=(-)^{(p-1)/2}
;

4.

The second supplement is
(2|p)=(-)^{(p^{2}-1)/8}
;

5.

For arbitrary
m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have
(mn|p)=(m|p)(n|p)
;

The proof proceeds as follows. Item 1 follows from the fact that the map
x\mapsto x^{2}
is two-to-one in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{*}
.
For Item 2, we first note that Fermat’s little theorem, namely that
n^{p-1}\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}p)
for any
n
not divisible by
p
, implies
n^{(p-1)/2}\equiv\pm 1~({\rm mod~}p)
. For
(n|p)=1
there exists an
x
so that
x^{2}\equiv n~({\rm mod~}p)
and hence
n^{(p-1)/2}=x^{p-1}\equiv 1=(n|p)~({\rm mod~}p)
. For
(n|p)=-1
the degree
(p-1)/2
polynomial congruence equation
x^{(p-1)/2}-1\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
has
(p-1)/2
solutions. The quadratic residues already provide
(p-1)/2
solutions, so the non-residues cannot be solutions. As a result, we have
x^{(p-1)/2}\not\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}p)
and hence
x^{(p-1)/2}\equiv-1=(n|p)~({\rm mod~}p)
. Finally, Items 3, 4, and 5 follow from Item 2. Item 3 may also be derived from Fermat’s theorem that a prime
p
is the sum of the squares of two integers if and only if
p\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}4)
.

The Jacobi symbol
(n|N)
generalizes the Legendre symbol to an arbitrary positive odd integer
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
where
p_{i}
are distinct primes and
\alpha_{i}\geq 1
and any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
by,

\displaystyle(n|N)=\prod_{i}(n|p_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}

(A.4.3)

where in each factor
(n|p_{i})
is the Legendre symbol. The Kronecker symbol further generalizes the Jacobi symbol by lifting the restrictions that
N
be odd and positive, and is defined as follows. If
N=u\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
where
u=\mp 1
and the product runs over all distinct primes including 2, then the Kronecker symbol is defined by,

\displaystyle(n|N)=(n|u)\prod_{i}(n|p_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}

(A.4.4)

where
(n|p_{i})
stands for the Legendre symbol when
p_{i}
is an odd prime, while the remaining data are supplied by,

\displaystyle(n|-1)=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&\hbox{ if }n\geq 0\cr-1&\hbox{ if }n<0\cr\end{matrix}\right.\hskip 65.04256pt(n|2)=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&\hbox{ if }n\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}2)~~\cr+1&\hbox{ if }n\equiv\pm 1~({\rm mod~}8)\cr-1&\hbox{ if }n\equiv\pm 3~({\rm mod~}8)\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(A.4.5)

The Legendre, Jacobi, and Kronecker symbols are referred to as quadratic residue symbols.

A.4.1

Quadratic residues
x^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)

We now analyze the particular example of quadratic residues of
-1
modulo
N
. This example, as well as that in the following subsection, will be used extensively when we discuss the dimensions and genera of congruence subgroups.

To obtain the number of solutions of the congruence
x^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
for an arbitrary integer
N
with prime factorization
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
we consider the polynomial
f(x)=x^{2}+1
with
f^{\prime}(x)=2x
. If
p
is an odd prime, and
0\leq r<p
solves
r^{2}+1\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
then
r\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
. As a result,
f^{\prime}(r)=2r\not\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
so that there is a unique lift of
r
to
({\rm mod~}p^{2})
, and to any
({\rm mod~}p^{\alpha})
. If
p=2
then
r\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}2)
so there is one solution
({\rm mod~}2)
. Now using Theorem
A.3
,
f^{\prime}(r)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}2)
, and the fact that every lift
f(1+2q)\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}4)
implies that there are no solutions
({\rm mod~}4)
, and hence no solutions
({\rm mod~}2^{\alpha})
for all
\alpha\geq 2
. Hence the number of solutions to
x^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
is given by,

\displaystyle\#\Big\{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\,\big|\,x^{2}\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)\Big\}=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&&4|N\cr\prod_{i}\Big(1+(-1|p_{i})\Big)&&4\nmid N\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(A.4.6)

where the Legendre symbol
(-1|p)
evaluates to
(-1)^{{p-1\over 2}}
for an odd prime
p
. For
p=2
, the number of solutions is 1, so we set
(-1|2)=0
.

A.4.2

Quadratic residues
x^{2}+x\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)

To obtain the number of solutions of the congruence
x^{2}+x\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)
for an arbitrary integer
N
with prime factorization
N=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
we consider the polynomial
f(x)=x^{2}+x+1
with
f^{\prime}(x)=2x+1
.
Solving
f(x)\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}N)
is equivalent to solving,

\displaystyle(2x+1)^{2}+3\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}4N)

(A.4.7)

If
9|N
, then the congruence
y^{2}+3\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}9)
has no solutions as the quadratic residues
{\rm mod~}9
are
0,1,4,7
, giving the residues of
y^{2}+3
to be
3,4,7,1
but not 0. For an odd prime
p
, the congruence
y^{2}+3\equiv 0~({\rm mod~}p)
has
1+(-3|p)
solutions. By quadratic reciprocity, if
p\not=3
then
(-3|p)=(-1|p)(3|p)=(p|3)
which evaluates to
\pm 1
when
p\equiv\pm 1~({\rm mod~}3)
. For
p\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}3)
there are no solutions
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and hence no solution of the form
y\equiv 2x+1~({\rm mod~}p)
. For
p\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}3)
there are two solutions
y~({\rm mod~}p)
. For any given
y
, there is then a unique solution
x
to
y\equiv 2x+1~({\rm mod~}p)
.
The number of solutions is thus given by,

\displaystyle\#\Big\{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}\,\big|\,x^{2}+x\equiv-1~({\rm mod~}N)\Big\}=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&&9|N\cr\prod_{i}\Big(1+(-3|p_{i})\Big)&&9\nmid N\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(A.4.8)

For
p=3
the number of solutions is 1, so we set
(-3|3)=0
.

A.5

Gauss sums

For an odd prime
p
, and an arbitrary
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the Gauss sum
g_{a}
is defined by,

\displaystyle g_{a}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(k|p)\,\varepsilon^{ka}\hskip 72.26999pt\varepsilon=e^{2\pi i/p}

(A.5.1)

Gauss sums are directly related to the Legendre symbol,

\displaystyle g_{a}=(a|p)g\hskip 72.26999ptg=g_{1}

(A.5.2)

Indeed, for
a
divisible by
p
we have
g_{a}=0
in view of the fact that the number of residues in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{*}
equals the number of non-residues. Hence the relation holds trivially when
p|a
as both sides vanish. For
p\nmid a
,
we use the fact that
(a|p)^{2}=1
, the definition of
g_{a}
, and the complete multiplication property of the Legendre symbol,

\displaystyle g_{a}=(a|p)\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(a|p)(k|p)\,\varepsilon^{ka}=(a|p)\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(ka|p)\,\varepsilon^{ka}=(a|p)g

(A.5.3)

The last equality follows, for
p\nmid a
, from the fact that the sum over
k
is identical to the sum over
ka
. Furthermore, we have the following relation,

\displaystyle g^{2}=(-)^{(p-1)/2}p

(A.5.4)

which follows from
g_{a}g_{-a}=(-1|p)g^{2}
and expressing its sum over
a
in two different ways,

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{p-1}g_{a}g_{-a}=(p-1)(-1|p)g^{2}=\sum_{a,k,\ell=0}^{p-1}(k\ell|p)\varepsilon^{(k-\ell)a}=p\sum_{k,\ell=0}^{p-1}(k\ell|p)\delta_{k,\ell}=p(p-1)

(A.5.5)

where
\delta_{k,\ell}=1
when
k=\ell
and zero otherwise. The result (
A.5.4
) follows upon using the first supplement
(-1|p)=(-)^{(p-1)/2}
.

A.6

Quadratic reciprocity

Quadratic reciprocity states that for
p,q
distinct odd primes, we have,

\displaystyle(p|q)(q|p)=(-)^{(p-1)(q-1)/4}

(A.6.1)

Many proofs exist of the law of quadratic reciprocity, of which Gauss produced at least 8. Here we shall use Gauss’s sums as defined in the preceding subsection. For any odd prime
q
not equal to
p
, we begin by using (
A.5.4
) to write,

\displaystyle g^{q-1}=(g^{2})^{(q-1)/2}=(g^{2}|q)=(-1|q)^{(p-1)/2}(p|q)=(-)^{(p-1)(q-1)/2}(p|q)

(A.6.2)

where we used the relation
(n|p)\equiv n^{(p-1)/2}~({\rm mod~}p)
of property 2. of
A.4
in the second equality. Next, we use the fact that the prime power of a multiple sum evaluates as follows,

\displaystyle(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n})^{q}=a_{1}^{q}+\cdots a_{n}^{q}~({\rm mod~}q)

(A.6.3)

This relation is valid for
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, but we generalize it here to the ring of algebraic integers
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}(\varepsilon)
. As a result we have,

\displaystyle g^{q}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(k|p)\varepsilon^{k}\right)^{q}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(k|p)^{q}\varepsilon^{kq}=\sum^{p-1}_{k=0}(k|p)\varepsilon^{kq}=g_{q}~({\rm mod~}q)

(A.6.4)

By (
A.5.2
), we have
g_{q}=(q|p)g
, and upon combining with (
A.6.2
), we get,

\displaystyle g^{q-1}=(-)^{(p-1)(q-1)|2}(p|q)=(q|p)

(A.6.5)

The last equality implies the law of quadratic reciprocity upon using
(p|q)^{2}=1
.

A.7

Characters

Consider a finite Abelian group
G
. A function
\chi:G\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is said to be a character if it is a group homomorphism, i.e.
\chi(gh)=\chi(g)\chi(h)
for all
g,h\in G
. Characters form a group
\hat{G}={\rm Hom}(G,{\mathbb{C}}^{*})
, known as the Poincaré dual of
G
. We will denote the identity element of this group as
\chi_{0}
, such that
\chi_{0}(g)=1
for all
g
. The inverse elements are obtained by complex conjugation
\chi^{-1}(g)=\overline{\chi(g)}
.

As an example, consider the case of a cyclic group
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
. If we denote the order-
n
generator by
v
, then it is clear that
\chi(v)^{n}=\chi(v^{n})=1
and hence we can take
\chi(v)
to be an
n
-th root of unity. Conversely, every
n
-th root of unity defines a character of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
. Thus we conclude that
\hat{G}
is in fact isomorphic to the original group,
\hat{G}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
.

Characters satisfy the following relations

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}|G|&\chi=\chi_{0}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\hat{G}}\chi(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}|\hat{G}|&g=\mathds{1}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

(A.7.1)

The first line of each of these is obvious. To prove the second line of the first relation, begin by choosing
h\in G
such that
\chi(h)\neq 1
. Then we have

\displaystyle\chi(h)\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(hg)=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)

(A.7.2)

where we have used the fact that
\chi
is a homomorphism, as well as closure of the group
G
. Since
\chi(h)\neq 1
, we conclude
\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)=0
as per the claim. The second relation is proven exactly analogously—namely we multiply by
\chi^{\prime}(g)\neq 1
and used the fact that
\hat{G}
is a group.

From these results we may obtain the following Schur orthogonality relations

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\overline{\chi^{\prime}(g)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}|G|&\chi=\chi^{\prime}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\hat{G}}\chi(g)\overline{\chi(h)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}|\hat{G}|&g=h\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

(A.7.3)

Indeed, for the former note that if
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\hat{G}
then
\chi(\chi^{\prime})^{-1}\in\hat{G}
as well. We may thus use the relation (
A.7
) with
\chi
replaced by
\chi(\chi^{\prime})^{-1}
. Then noting that,

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}(\chi(\chi^{\prime})^{-1})(g)=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\overline{\chi^{\prime}(g)}

(A.7.4)

we obtain the desired result. Likewise for the latter relation, we use the fact that,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\hat{G}}\chi(gh^{-1})=\sum_{\chi\in\hat{G}}\chi(g)\overline{\chi(h)}

(A.7.5)

This completes the proof of both relations in (
A.7
).

A.8

Dirichlet characters and L-functions

A Dirichlet character for
a\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a function
\chi_{a}:{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying the following properties:

1.

Periodicity:
\chi_{a}(n)=\chi_{a}(n+a)
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

2.

\chi_{a}(n)\neq 0
if and only if
\gcd(n,a)=1
.

3.

Multiplicativity:
\chi_{a}(nm)=\chi_{a}(n)\chi_{a}(m)
for all
n,m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Dirichlet characters can be thought of as characters for
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{a}^{*}
, the multiplicative group of invertible elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{a}
. This is an Abelian group with
|G|=\phi(a)
, where
\phi
is Euler’s totient function. In this case our orthogonality relations read as follows,

\displaystyle\sum_{g=0}^{a-1}\chi_{a}(g)\overline{\chi^{\prime}_{a}(g)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}\phi(a)&\chi_{a}=\chi^{\prime}_{a}\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi_{a}\in\hat{G}}\chi_{a}(g)\overline{\chi_{a}(h)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}\phi(a)&g\equiv h~({\rm mod~}a)\\
0&{\rm otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.

(A.8.1)

We now introduce the Dirichlet L-function,

\displaystyle L_{a}(s,\chi)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(n)\over n^{s}}

(A.8.2)

For
\chi_{a}
not equal to the identity character,
L_{a}(s,\chi)
converges and is analytic for
{\rm Re}\,s>1

[
23
,
128
]
. In this region, we have the following Euler product formula,

\displaystyle L_{a}(s,\chi)=\prod_{p}{1\over 1-{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}}

(A.8.3)

the product being over prime numbers. To prove this, note that for each prime
p
, we have,

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{a}(p)p^{-s})^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\chi_{a}(p)^{n}p^{-ns}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\chi_{a}(p^{n})p^{-ns}

(A.8.4)

Hence for fixed prime
q
, we have,

\displaystyle\prod_{p\leq q}{1\over 1-{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}}=\sum_{n\in S_{q}}{\chi_{a}(n)\over n^{s}}

(A.8.5)

with
S_{q}
the set of all natural numbers whose prime factors are less than or equal to
q
. Then for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we conclude that,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\chi_{a}(n)\over n^{s}}=\prod_{p\leq q}{1\over 1-{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in S_{q}\\
n\geq N\end{subarray}}{\chi_{a}(n)\over n^{s}}

(A.8.6)

with
q
the largest prime less than or equal to
N
. Taking
N\rightarrow\infty
gives (
A.8.3
).

Using this product formula, it is possible to show that
L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})
extends to a meromorphic function on
{\rm Res}\,s>0
, with the only pole at
s=1
. We do not reproduce the proof here; it can be found in e.g.
[
23
,
128
]
.

A.8.1

Dirichlet’s Theorem on Arithmetic Progressions

We now use Dirichlet L-functions to prove Dirichlet’s Theorem on arithmetic progressions,

Theorem 1

Let
m
and
a
be relatively prime natural numbers. Then there are infinitely many prime numbers
p
such that
p\equiv m~({\rm mod~}a)
.

In order to prove this, we aim to prove the following stronger result,

\displaystyle\sum_{p\equiv m~({\rm mod~}a)}{1\over p}\hskip 14.45377pt\rm{is\,\,\,divergent}

(A.8.7)

which clearly implies the theorem. To do so, begin by considering the logarithm of the Dirichlet L-function, which by the product formula takes the form

\displaystyle\log L_{a}(s,\chi)=\sum_{p}\left(-\log\left(1-{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}\right)\right)

(A.8.8)

valid for
{\rm Re}\,s>1
. Taylor expanding the logarithm, this gives

\displaystyle\log L_{a}(s,\chi)=\sum_{p}{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}+\sum_{p}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(p)^{n}\over np^{ns}}

(A.8.9)

where, for reasons to be clear in a moment, we have split the
n=1
term from the rest. Using the orthogonality of Dirichlet characters in (
A.8
), we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{p\equiv m~({\rm mod~}a)}{1\over p^{s}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over\phi(a)}\sum_{\chi_{a}\in\hat{G}}\overline{\chi_{a}(m)}\sum_{p}{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}

(A.8.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over\phi(a)}\left[\sum_{\chi_{a}\in\hat{G}}\overline{\chi_{a}(m)}\log L_{a}(s,\chi)-\sum_{\chi_{a}\in\hat{G}}\overline{\chi_{a}(m)}\sum_{p}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(p)^{n}\over np^{ns}}\right]

where
(a,m)=1
lest
\chi_{a}(m)=0
. To prove the theorem, we must now show that the right-hand side is divergent as
s\rightarrow 1
. To do so, we begin by showing that
\sum_{p}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(p)^{n}\over np^{ns}}
is bounded as
s\rightarrow 1
, and hence that the second term in the difference does not diverge. To see this, note that since
|\chi_{a}(p)|=0
or 1, we have
|\chi_{a}(p)p^{-s}|\leq|p^{-s}|\leq{1\over 2}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(p)^{n}\over np^{ns}}\right|\leq\left|{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}\right|^{2}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{1\over n}\left|{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}\right|^{n-2}\leq\left|{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}\right|^{2}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{1\over 2}{1\over 2^{n-2}}=\left|{\chi_{a}(p)\over p^{s}}\right|^{2}\leq{1\over p^{2}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

(A.8.11)

Hence we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{p}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}{\chi_{a}(p)^{n}\over np^{ns}}\right|\leq\sum_{p}{1\over p^{2}}<\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over n^{2}}

(A.8.12)

the latter of which is convergent.

Having shown that the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.8.10
) is not divergent as
s\rightarrow 1
, we are now tasked with showing that the first term is divergent. At the end of appendix
A.8
we quoted the result that
L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})\rightarrow\infty
as
s\rightarrow 1
, and hence
\log L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})
diverges in this limit as well. To complete the proof it thus suffices to show that
L_{a}(1,\chi)\neq 0
for all
\chi\neq\chi_{0}
.

To show this, first consider a character
\chi_{a}
such that
\chi_{a}^{2}
is not the identity character. Then we can consider the function

\displaystyle\lambda(s)=L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})^{3}L_{a}(s,\chi)^{4}L_{a}(s,\chi^{2})

(A.8.13)

By (
A.8.9
) we have

\displaystyle|\lambda(s)|=\left|{\rm exp}\left[\sum_{p}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{3+4\chi_{a}(p^{n})+\chi_{a}^{2}(p^{n})\over np^{ns}}\right]\right|

(A.8.14)

Letting
\theta_{n,p}={\rm Arg}\,\chi_{a}(p^{n})
, we then obtain

\displaystyle|\lambda(s)|={\rm exp}\left|\sum_{p}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{3+4\cos\theta_{n,p}+\cos 2\theta_{n,p}\over np^{n\,{\rm Re}\,s}}\right|

(A.8.15)

But for any real
\theta
, we have

\displaystyle 3+4\cos\theta+2\cos\theta=2(1+\cos\theta)^{2}\geq 0

(A.8.16)

Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle|\lambda(s)|\geq e^{0}=1

(A.8.17)

This gives the desired result. Indeed, if we had
L_{a}(1,\chi)=0
, then since
L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})
has a simple pole at
s=1
but
L_{a}(s,\chi^{2})
does not (because
\chi_{a}^{2}\neq\chi_{0}
by assumption) the degree
\geq 4
zero wins against the degree 3 pole, and we would have
\lambda(1)=0
, in contradiction to the inequality just obtained. Thus we must have
L_{a}(1,\chi)\neq 0
.

For the case of
\chi_{a}^{2}=\chi_{0}
, a similar argument can be run using
\lambda(s)=L_{a}(s,\chi)L_{a}(s,\chi_{0})
. In this case one aims to show that
\log\lambda(s)\geq\log\zeta(2s)
. In total, one concludes that
L_{a}(1,\chi)\neq 0
for all
\chi\neq\chi_{0}
, completing the proof of the theorem.

A.8.2

Perfect squares and quadratic residues

We close by using the various tools of this section, including Dirichlet’s theorem of arithmetic progressions, to prove the following statement:

Theorem 2

A number
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a perfect square if and only if it is a quadratic residue for all primes
p
coprime to
N
.

One direction of this statement is obvious: if
N
can be written as a perfect square, it is clear that it is also a perfect square modulo any prime. We now focus on the other direction. Assume that
N
is a quadratic residue modulo all coprime
p
. Say that
N
is not a perfect square. We may then write
N
in the form
N=j^{2}p_{1}\dots p_{r}
for distinct primes
p_{1},\dots p_{r}
. Since any odd power of 2 is a quadratic non-residue mod 3, we may assume that there is at least one odd factor.
Take
p_{1}
to be odd and consider non-zero integers
m
and
n_{i}
such that
(m|p_{1})=-1
and
(n_{i}|p_{i})=1
for
i=2,\dots,r
. By the Chinese remainder theorem, there exists a solution to the following set of congruences

\displaystyle x\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}4)\hskip 36.135ptx\equiv m~({\rm mod~}p_{1})\hskip 36.135ptx\equiv n_{i}~({\rm mod~}p_{i})\hskip 36.135ptp_{i}\neq 2

(A.8.18)

If one of the prime factors
p_{i}=2
, then we replace the final congruence with
x\equiv 1~({\rm mod~}8)
. Given a particular solution
x_{0}
to these congruences, the general solution takes the form
x=x_{0}+4kp_{1}\dots p_{r}
for integer
k
. By Dirichlet’s theorem of arithmetic progressions this takes an infinite number of prime values, and we choose one, say
x=p
, such that
p\nmid j
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle(p|p_{1})=(m|p_{1})=-1\hskip 57.81621pt(p|p_{i})=(n_{i}|p_{i})=1\hskip 36.135pti=2,\dots,r

(A.8.19)

Then using quadratic reciprocity, together with the first congruence of (
A.8.18
), we conclude that

\displaystyle(p_{1}|p)=-1\hskip 57.81621pt{\rm and}\hskip 57.81621pt(p_{i}|p)=1\hskip 21.68121pti=2,\dots,r

(A.8.20)

Thus
(N|p)=(p_{1}|p)\dots(p_{r}|p)=-1
, and we have identified a prime
p
coprime to
N
for which
N
is not a quadratic residue, contrary to hypothesis. We conclude that
N
must be a perfect square.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

An excellent overview of some classic problems in arithmetic is given in the book by Cox
[
144
]
, where a historical perspective is also provided. Two other classics are the books by Serre
[
128
]
and Hecke
[
337
]
. Useful references for results in analytic number theory, including
L
-functions, may be found in the book by Iwaniec
[
23
]
, Rademacher
[
46
]
, and Siegel
[
53
]
.

Appendix B

Riemann surfaces

A Riemann surface is a connected complex manifold of two real dimensions or equivalently a connected complex manifold of one complex dimension, also referred to as a complex curve. In this appendix, we shall review the topology of Riemann surfaces, their homology groups, homotopy groups, uniformization, construction in terms of Fuchsian groups, as well as their emergence from two-dimensional orientable Riemannian manifolds.

B.1

Topology

The topology of a Riemann surface
\Sigma
is the topology of the underlying orientable two-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
. Throughout, we shall restrict to Riemann surfaces whose boundary
\partial\Sigma
is the union of a finite number
b
of one-dimensional components, and a finite number
n
of points referred to as punctures of
\Sigma
. Given a triangulation of
\Sigma
with
F_{\Sigma}
faces,
E_{\Sigma}
edges, and
V_{\Sigma}
vertices, the Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma)
is defined by,

\displaystyle\chi(\Sigma)=F_{\Sigma}-E_{\Sigma}+V_{\Sigma}

(B.1.1)

The Euler characteristic is a topological invariant in the sense that its value is independent of the triangulation chosen for a given
\Sigma
. In suitably chosen triangulations, the operation of adding a puncture to
\Sigma
(namely removing a point from
\Sigma
) leaves
F_{\Sigma}
and
E_{\Sigma}
unchanged but diminishes
V_{\Sigma}
by one while adding a one-dimensional boundary component leaves
E_{\Sigma}
and
V_{\Sigma}
unchanged but diminishes
F_{\Sigma}
by one. The remaining contribution to
\chi(\Sigma)
derived from the genus
g
, which is the number of handles on
\Sigma
, is,

\displaystyle\chi(\Sigma)=2-2g-n-b

(B.1.2)

A Riemann surface with
b\not=0
may be studied by considering its double, which is obtained by gluing
\Sigma
and its mirror image
\Sigma^{\prime}
into a Riemann surface
\hat{\Sigma}
without one-dimensional boundary components. The surface
\hat{\Sigma}
is endowed with an involution
\iota
which swaps
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Henceforth, we shall restrict attention to surfaces with
b=0
.

Compact Riemann surfaces play a central role in both Mathematics and Physics. It will often be useful to consider the generalization to a compact Riemann surface with
n
punctures defined to be a compact Riemann surface with a finite number
n
of points removed, namely with
n
punctures. Their study may be initiated by first studying the underlying compact surface and then removing
n
points.

B.1.1

The Riemann-Hurwitz formula

Holomorphic maps between Riemann surfaces allow us to relate different Riemann surfaces.
We begin by considering a non-constant holomorphic map
f:X\to Y
between two compact Riemann surfaces
X
and
Y
. The degree of
f
is the multiplicity of the function
f^{-1}
given by the cardinality
d=|f^{-1}(y)|
for all but finitely many points
y\in Y
. A formula valid for all points in
Y
is obtained in terms of the degree of ramification
e_{x}
of
f
at a point
x\in X
which, in local coordinates, is given by
f(z)-f(x)\sim(z-x)^{e_{x}}
. The degree of
f
may then be defined for all
y\in Y
by the following formula,

\displaystyle d=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}e_{x}

(B.1.3)

and
d
is independent of
y
. The Riemann-Hurwitz formula relates the genera
g_{X}
and
g_{Y}
of the surfaces
X
and
Y
to the degree of
f
and the deviations from 1 of the degree of ramification,

\displaystyle 2g_{X}-2=d(2g_{Y}-2)+\sum_{x\in X}(e_{x}-1)

(B.1.4)

A hyper-elliptic Riemann surface
X
, viewed as a double cover of the Riemann sphere
Y
, provides an illuminating example of the Riemann-Hurwitz formula for
d=2
since the presence of
2n
branch points at which
e_{x}=2
readily gives
g_{X}=n-1
. The formula (
B.1.4
) may be proven by using the expression for the Euler characteristic
\chi(X)
in (
B.1.1
) of
X
in terms of a triangulation by
F_{X}
faces,
E_{X}
edges, and
V_{X}
vertices (including all the ramification points), and similarly for
\chi(Y)
. Given that the degree of the map is
d
, we have
F_{X}=dF_{Y}
and
E_{X}=dE_{Y}
but
V_{X}=dV_{Y}-\sum_{x\in X}(e_{x}-1)
since a vertex with ramification degree
e_{x}
counts for only one vertex of
X
. Expressing the result in terms of the Euler numbers we have
\chi(X)=d\chi(Y)-\sum_{x\in X}(e_{x}-1)
. For a compact Riemann surface without boundary or punctures, the Euler numbers are related to the genus by
\chi(X)=2-2g_{X}
and
\chi(Y)=2-2g_{Y}
from which (
B.1.4
) follows.
37
37

37

For the special case of an un-ramified covering the formula
\chi(X)=d\chi(Y)
for
\chi<0
the formula readily follows from the fact that the Euler number is proportional to the area for unit negative curvature metrics.

B.1.2

Homotopy

The homotopy groups
\pi_{0}(\Sigma)
and
\pi_{2}(\Sigma)
for a compact Riemann surface are trivial, and the interesting homotopy resides entirely in the first homotopy group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, which we now define.

A closed curve
\mathfrak{C}:[0,1]\to\Sigma
is a continuous map such that
\mathfrak{C}(1)=\mathfrak{C}(0)
. Two closed curves
\mathfrak{C}_{0},\mathfrak{C}_{1}
are said to be homotopic (to one another) iff there exists a family of curves
\mathfrak{F}(s,t)
parametrized by
t\in[0,1]
, such that
\mathfrak{F}(s,t)\subset\Sigma
and is continuous in
s,t
for all
s,t\in[0,1]
and interpolates as follows:
\mathfrak{F}(s,0)={\cal C}_{0}(s)
and
\mathfrak{F}(s,1)=\mathfrak{C}_{1}(s)
. The homotopy relation amongst closed curves is clearly an equivalence relation, whose classes are the first homotopy classes. To make the set of classes into a group under composition of maps, we choose a base-point
P\in\Sigma
and restrict to curves in each class that pass through
P
. Thus, we may choose the parametrization of each closed curve
\mathfrak{C}
that passes through
P
so that
\mathfrak{C}(0)=\mathfrak{C}(1)=P
. The composition
\mathfrak{C}_{1}\circ\mathfrak{C}_{0}
of two curves
\mathfrak{C}_{0},\mathfrak{C}_{1}
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{1}\circ\mathfrak{C}_{0}(s)=\left\{\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{C}_{0}(2s)&\hbox{ for }&s\in[0,{1\over 2}]\cr\mathfrak{C}_{1}(2s-1)&\hbox{ for }&s\in[{1\over 2},1]\end{matrix}\right.

(B.1.5)

The composition of closed curves induces a composition of first homotopy classes of curves, where
the identity element
e
is the class of contractile curves, and the inverse is the class of curves with opposite orientation. The resulting group of classes is independent of the base-point
P

38
38

38

Independence of
P
requires
\Sigma
to be path-connected as we shall assume throughout.
and is referred to as the first homotopy group, or fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

Proposition B.1

The Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
, the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
, the Poincaré upper half-plane
{\cal H}
, and the unit disc
{\cal D}
are simply connected Riemann surfaces, as is any compact Riemann surface
\Sigma_{0}
of genus 0, so that their first homotopy groups are given by,

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\hat{\mathbb{C}})=\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}})=\pi_{1}({\cal H})=\pi_{1}({\cal D})=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})=\{e\}

(B.1.6)

For a compact surface
\Sigma
of genus 1, namely a torus, we have
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

For surfaces of genus
g\geq 2
, the fundamental group is non-Abelian, and will be discussed concretely in appendix
B.3.2
in terms of representations of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

\mathfrak{A}_{1}
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
\Sigma
\bullet
P

Figure 20
:
A choice of homotopy generators
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
with common base point
P
is illustrated for a compact genus-two Riemann surface
\Sigma
.

B.1.3

Homology

The top and bottom homology groups of a Riemann surface are trivial. All the interesting homology resides in the first homology group which, for a compact Riemann surface of genus
g
with
n
punctures, is given by
H_{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}^{2g+n}
. The primitive homology cycle associated with each puncture is homologous to a small circle centered at the puncture. Having chosen an orientation for the Riemann surface
\Sigma
, we introduce a binary intersection pairing
\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2})
on oriented 1-cycles
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, which counts the number of intersections of the 1-cycles
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
with
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
weighed by
\pm 1
factors for the relative orientation of the cycles at their intersection points. The pairing
\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2})
is odd under swapping
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}
.

Specializing to the case of a compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
without punctures, the intersection form
\mathfrak{J}
is non-degenerate, and we may choose a basis for
H_{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}^{2g}
consisting of homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{J}
for
I,J=1,\cdots,g
such that,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{A}_{J})

\displaystyle=\,\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{B}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{J})\,=0

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{A}_{I},\mathfrak{B}_{J})

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{J}(\mathfrak{B}_{I},\mathfrak{A}_{J})=\delta_{IJ}

(B.1.7)

Two different canonical bases
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
and
(\tilde{\mathfrak{A}},\tilde{\mathfrak{B}})
are related by a linear transformation that may be represented by a matrix
M
with integer entries,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{\mathfrak{B}}\cr\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}\cr\end{matrix}\right)=M\left(\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{B}\cr\mathfrak{A}\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(B.1.8)

Here,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are column matrices with entries
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
, respectively. The matrix
M
is an element of the rank
g
modular group
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
which preserves the intersection matrix
\mathfrak{J}
,

\displaystyle M^{t}\mathfrak{J}M=\mathfrak{J}\hskip 50.58878pt\mathfrak{J}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-I_{g}\cr I_{g}&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 50.58878ptM=\left(\begin{matrix}A&B\cr C&D\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(B.1.9)

where
A,B,C,D
are
g\times g
matrices with integer entries. An important subgroup of
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
is the group
GL(g,{\mathbb{Z}})
which consists of those
M
that transform
\mathfrak{A}
-cycles into linear combinations of
\mathfrak{A}
-cycles and
\mathfrak{B}
-cycles into linear combinations of
\mathfrak{B}
-cycles. It is obtained by setting
B=C=0
and
D^{-1}=A^{t}
.

B.1.4

Cohomology

By the de Rham theorem, the first de Rham cohomology group is given by
H^{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}^{2g}
and is generated by
2g
real-valued harmonic 1-forms on
\Sigma
. Since
\Sigma
is a complex manifold, harmonic one-forms may be decomposed into holomorphic and anti-holomorphic one-forms, and the cohomology may be decomposed into the Dolbeault cohomology groups,

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})=H^{(1,0)}(\Sigma)\oplus H^{(0,1)}(\Sigma)

(B.1.10)

generated by holomorphic
(1,0)
forms and anti-holomorphic
(0,1)
forms respectively. A canonical basis of the Dolbeault cohomology group
H^{(1,0)}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}^{g}
consists of holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms
\omega_{I}
with
I=1,\cdots,g
whose periods are normalized on the cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
of the canonical homology basis
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
, as follows,

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}\omega_{J}=\delta_{IJ}

(B.1.11)

The periods on the remaining 1-cycles are then completely determined and give the matrix elements of the period matrix
\Omega
of
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}\omega_{J}=\Omega_{IJ}

(B.1.12)

Under modular transformations
M\in Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
, whose parametrization in terms of
g\times g
matrices
A,B,C,D
is given in (
B.1.9
), the matrix of holomorphic Abelian differentials
\omega
, the period matrix
\Omega
, and its imaginary part
Y
, transform as follows,

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(C\Omega+D)^{-1}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(A\Omega+B)(C\Omega+D)^{-1}

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Omega C^{t}+D^{t})^{-1}Y(C\Omega^{*}+D)

(B.1.13)

Clearly, the transformation formula for
\Omega
reduces to the standard Möbius transformation formula for the special case of
g=1
.

B.1.5

Riemann bilinear relations

A useful formula relates the integral over
\Sigma
of a wedge product of closed forms
\varpi_{1}
and
\varpi_{2}
to their line integrals over canonical homology cycles,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\varpi_{1}\wedge\varpi_{2}=\sum_{K=1}^{g}\left(\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{K}}\varpi_{1}\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{K}}\varpi_{2}-\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{K}}\varpi_{1}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{K}}\varpi_{2}\right)

(B.1.14)

The formula may be established by cutting the surface
\Sigma
along homology cycles
\mathfrak{A}_{K}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{K}
for
K=1,\cdots,g
, chosen so as to have a point in common, into a simply-connected domain
\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}
in the plane, as illustrated in Figure
21
. Expressing one of the closed differentials as an exact differential of a local function in
\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}
, for example,
\varpi_{2}=df_{2}
, the formula may be derived using Green’s theorem for an arbitrary 1-form
\varpi
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}}d\varpi=\oint_{\partial\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}}\varpi

(B.1.15)

where the boundary consists of the union of cycles,

\displaystyle\partial\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}=\bigcup_{I}\Big(\mathfrak{A}_{I}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{I}\cup\mathfrak{A}_{I}^{-1}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{I}^{-1}\Big)

(B.1.16)

as illustrated in Figure
21
for genus
g=2
.

The period matrix
\Omega
is symmetric by the Riemann bilinear relations obtained by setting
\varpi_{1}=\omega_{I}
and
\varpi_{2}=\omega_{J}
in (
B.1.14
). Furthermore, the imaginary part of the period matrix,

\displaystyle Y={\rm Im\,}\,\Omega

(B.1.17)

is positive definite by the Riemann relations obtained by setting
\varpi_{1}=\omega_{I}
and
\varpi_{2}=\bar{\omega}_{J}
in (
B.1.14
), and using the fact that
\tfrac{i}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\varpi\wedge\varpi^{*}
is positive definite for holomorphic 1-forms
\varpi
.

\mathfrak{A}_{1}
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}_{2}

Representation of the Riemann surface
\Sigma

\Sigma_{{\rm cut}}

\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
with common base point
P
