\Sigma
.

B.2

Metrics and complex structures

A different point of view on Riemann surfaces, with or without punctures, is obtained by considering connected two-dimensional orientable manifolds endowed with a Riemannian metric. This point of view is important in Physics and is widely regarded as the starting point for string perturbation theory in the Polyakov formulation. Consider a two-dimensional oriented manifold
\Sigma
endowed with a Riemannian metric
\mathfrak{g}
. In a system of local real coordinates
\xi^{m}=(\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
the metric takes the form,
39
39

39

Throughout, the Einstein convention is used to sum over a pair of repeated upper and lower indices.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)d\xi^{m}d\xi^{n}

(B.2.1)

We review the following notions:

•

A diffeomorphism is a smooth map
\xi\to\xi^{\prime}(\xi)
under which
\mathfrak{g}
is invariant, and the components
\mathfrak{g}_{mn}
transform as follows,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{mn}(\xi^{\prime}){\partial\xi^{\prime\,m}\over\partial\xi^{p}}{\partial\xi^{\prime\,n}\over\partial\xi^{q}}=\mathfrak{g}_{pq}(\xi)

(B.2.2)

Diffeomorphisms form an infinite-dimensional group
{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)
.

•

A Weyl transformation rescales the metric
\mathfrak{g}
by an arbitrary positive function
\lambda
on
\Sigma
, namely
\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)\to\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{mn}(\xi)=\lambda(\xi)\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)
. Weyl transformations form an infinite-dimensional group
{\rm Weyl}(\Sigma)
.

•

Generally, a conformal transformation preserves all angles. All Weyl transformations are conformal transformations in this sense. A conformal coordinate transformation, or conformal diffeomorphism is a diffeomorphism which is conformal, which requires that
\mathfrak{g}_{mn}^{\prime}(\xi^{\prime})=\mu(\xi)\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)
for some positive real function
\mu
.

•

An isometry of
\mathfrak{g}
preserves all distances. A diffeomorphism is an isometry of
\mathfrak{g}
provided
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{mn}(\xi^{\prime})=\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)
. Every conformal diffeomorphism is an isometry, but there exist conformal transformations, such as scaling by a constant, that are not isometries.

We have the following theorem,

Theorem B.2

An orientable connected two-dimensional surface
\Sigma
endowed with a Riemannian metric is automatically a Riemann surface.

To establish this result, we show that the combination of orientability and the presence of a Riemannian metric induces a unique complex structure. The orientation may be specified by choosing an oriented volume form,

\displaystyle d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sqrt{{\rm det\,}\mathfrak{g}}\,\varepsilon_{mn}\,d\xi^{m}\wedge d\xi^{n}\hskip 72.26999pt\varepsilon_{mn}=-\varepsilon_{nm},~\varepsilon_{12}=+1

(B.2.3)

A reversal of orientation corresponds to reversing the sign of
\varepsilon
. We denote by
T_{p}(\Sigma)
the tangent space to
\Sigma
at the point
p\in\Sigma
, and by
T^{*}_{p}(\Sigma)
the cotangent space. A convenient basis is given by the partial derivatives
\partial_{m}=\partial/\partial\xi^{m}
for
T_{p}(\Sigma)
and by the differentials
d\xi^{m}
for
T^{*}_{p}(\Sigma)
. The tangent space
T_{p}(\Sigma)
may be viewed as the space of vectors
v^{m}\partial_{m}
at
p
, while the cotangent space may be viewed as the space of forms
\omega_{m}d\xi^{m}
at
p
. An almost complex structure
{\cal J}
is a map from
T_{p}(\Sigma)
to itself, or equivalently from
T^{*}_{p}(\Sigma)
to itself, whose square is minus the identity map,

\displaystyle{\cal J}:T_{p}(\Sigma)\to T_{p}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle v^{m}\to{\cal J}^{m}{}_{n}\,v^{n}\hskip 57.81621pt{\cal J}^{2}=-I

\displaystyle{\cal J}:T_{p}^{*}(\Sigma)\to T_{p}^{*}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\omega_{m}\to{\cal J}_{m}{}^{n}\,\omega_{n}

(B.2.4)

Having a metric and an orientation, it is straightforward to exhibit this map,

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{m}{}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\rm det\,}\mathfrak{g}}\,\varepsilon_{rn}\,\mathfrak{g}^{rm}

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{m}{}^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\rm det\,}\mathfrak{g}}\,\varepsilon_{mr}\,\mathfrak{g}^{rn}

(B.2.5)

The metric is a covariantly constant tensor with respect to the affine connection and its associated covariant derivative
\nabla_{s}
, so that we have
\nabla_{s}\mathfrak{g}_{mn}=0
. As a result,
{\cal J}
obeys
\nabla_{s}\,{\cal J}_{m}{}^{n}=0
and is therefore also a covariantly constant tensor acting on
T_{p}(\Sigma)
and
T^{*}_{p}(\Sigma)
. Furthermore,
{\cal J}
is invariant under arbitrary Weyl rescaling and therefore depends only on the conformal class of
\mathfrak{g}_{mn}
. Finally,
{\cal J}
is integrable in the sense that the system of equations,

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{m}{}^{n}\,\partial_{n}f=i\,\partial_{m}f

(B.2.6)

is integrable and thereby defines locally holomorphic functions
f
on
\Sigma
which endow
\Sigma
with a complex structure.
40
40

40

In dimensions higher than two, the system (
B.2.6
) has non-trivial integrability conditions, but in two dimensions there are none since the operator
{\cal J}-iI
has a one-dimensional null space.

We may use the solutions to (
B.2.6
) to define a system of local complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
in which the metric
\mathfrak{g}
is locally conformally flat,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}|dz|^{2}

(B.2.7)

for a real positive function
\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}
. The conformally flat form of the metric is invariant under locally holomorphic diffeomorphisms
z\to z^{\prime}(z)
. The complex manifold
\Sigma
may be constructed by covering
\Sigma
with coordinate charts
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
in each of which we have local complex coordinates
(z_{\alpha},\bar{z}_{\alpha})
. In the intersection
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap{\cal U}_{\beta}
of the two coordinate charts, the local coordinates are related by transition functions
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}(z)
which are holomorphic and nowhere vanishing via the relations
z_{\alpha}(z)=\varphi_{\alpha\beta}(z)z_{\beta}(z)
. Thus
\Sigma
is a Riemann surface.

B.2.1

The Euler characteristic in terms of a metric

The Riemann tensor on a connected two-dimensional Riemannian manifold has only one independent component, which may be expressed in terms of the Gaussian curvature
R_{\mathfrak{g}}
. The Euler characteristic of a compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
with metric
\mathfrak{g}
may be expressed in terms of
R_{\mathfrak{g}}
by the following classic formula,

\displaystyle\chi(\Sigma)={1\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\,R_{\mathfrak{g}}

(B.2.8)

The normalization of the Gaussian curvature
R_{\mathfrak{g}}
is such that the round sphere
S^{2}
of radius 1 has area
4\pi
and
R_{\mathfrak{g}}=1
, so that (
B.2.8
) indeed corresponds to genus zero. The Euler characteristic is a topological invariant. Indeed, the integral in (
B.2.8
) is independent of the metric
\mathfrak{g}
because
d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\,R_{\mathfrak{g}}
is locally an exact
(1,1)
-form.

B.3

Uniformization

In this subsection, we present the uniformization theorem for simply connected Riemann surfaces, and then use its results to produce concrete constructions for arbitrary compact Riemann surfaces with punctures.

Theorem B.3
(The uniformization theorem)

A simply connected Riemann surface is conformally isomorphic to either one of the following Riemann surfaces,

1.

The Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
which is the one-point compactification of
{\mathbb{C}}
(also denoted
S^{2}
);

2.

The complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
;

3.

The complex upper half-plane
{\cal H}
which is isomorphic to the unit disc
{\cal D}
.

For an arbitrary Riemannian metric
\mathfrak{g}
on
\Sigma
in each case, there exists a Weyl transformation to a metric
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=1
for
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
;
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=0
for
{\mathbb{C}}
; and
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=-1
for
{\cal H}
and
{\cal D}
. The corresponding metrics are given as follows,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{S^{2}}={4|dz|^{2}\over(1+|z|^{2})^{2}}\hskip 36.135pt\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}=|dz|^{2}\hskip 36.135pt\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\cal H}}={|dz|^{2}\over({\rm Im\,}z)^{2}}\hskip 36.135pt\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\cal D}}={4|dz|^{2}\over(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}

(B.3.1)

where
z\in\hat{\mathbb{C}}
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
{\rm Im\,}(z)>0
, and
|z|<1
respectively.

Each one of these spaces is maximally symmetric and may be expressed as the quotient of its isometry group by the isotropy group,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=SU(2)/U(1)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt{\mathbb{C}}=ISO(2)/SO(2)

\displaystyle{\cal H}

\displaystyle=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt{\cal D}=SU(1,1)/U(1)

(B.3.2)

The isometry groups
PSL(2,{\mathbb{R}})=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and
PSU(1,1)=SU(1,1)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
are isomorphic to one another. The action on the coordinate
z
in either case is given by,

\displaystyle z\to{az+b\over cz+d}

(B.3.3)

where
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
ad-bc=1
for
{\cal H}
and
c=b^{*},d=a^{*}
and
|a|^{2}-|b|^{2}=1
for
{\cal D}
. In both cases the elements
I
and
-I
leave
z
invariant and are identified under the center
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

The geodesics of
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
are the great circles; those of
{\mathbb{C}}
are straight lines; and those of
{\cal H}
are half-circles centered on the real axis. The geodesics of
{\cal D}
are the circle segments in
{\cal D}
, centered at any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
|\alpha|>1
, satisfying
|z-\alpha|^{2}=|\alpha|^{2}-1
. They intersect the unit circle
|z|=1
orthogonally to it. The system of geodesics of
{\cal H}
and
{\cal D}
makes these spaces into standard models for two-dimensional hyperbolic (or no-Euclidean) geometry.

i
\bullet
\infty
\bullet
0
-1
1
z\in{\cal H}
0
\bullet
\bullet
-1
\bullet
1
i
i
w\in{\cal D}

Figure 22
:
Geodesics in the upper half-plane
{\cal H}
are semi-circles centered on
{\mathbb{R}}
depicted in the left figure, with their corresponding images under the conformal map
w=(1+iz)/(1-iz)
from
{\cal H}
into the unit disc
{\cal D}
depicted in the right figure.

The uniformization theorem provides us with a powerful tool to classify and construct Riemann surfaces even when they are not simply connected. The key to the construction is the first homotopy group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, which is trivial for simply connected
\Sigma
but non-trivial when
\Sigma
is not simply-connected.

B.3.1

Genus one

Any compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
of genus 1 has
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
whose generators may be chosen to be the
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
cycles of the first homology group. The universal simply connected covering of a compact genus one surface
\Sigma
is the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
. The fundamental group is Abelian and acts on
{\mathbb{C}}
by translations via a representation
\rho:{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\mapsto{\mathbb{C}}
which defines a lattice
\Lambda
. To specify the representation, and the lattice
\Lambda
, it suffices to assign the periods
\rho(1,0)=\omega_{1}
and
\rho(0,1)=\omega_{2}
so that the lattice is given by
\Lambda=\omega_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\omega_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
. The lattice is two-dimensional (over
{\mathbb{R}}
) provided
\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}=\tau\in{\cal H}
. The action of the fundamental group, via the representation
\rho
on
{\mathbb{C}}
, is transitive and the quotient
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
is the original genus one compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
. Since translations in
{\mathbb{C}}
are isometries of the flat Euclidean metric
|dz|^{2}
on
{\mathbb{C}}
, the genus-one surface
{\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
inherits the flat metric
|dz|^{2}
subject to the identifications
z\approx z+\omega_{1}
and
z\approx z+\omega_{2}
.

B.3.2

Higher genus
g\geq 2

Theorem B.4

Every compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
admits a Riemannian metric
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
of constant negative curvature
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=-1
.

The curvature
R_{\mathfrak{g}}
of an arbitrary metric
\mathfrak{g}
on
\Sigma
is related to the curvature
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}
of a metric
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
related to
\mathfrak{g}
by a Weyl transformation
\mathfrak{g}=e^{2\sigma}\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
by the Liouville equation,

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{g}}=e^{-2\sigma}R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}+\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\sigma\hskip 72.26999pt\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\sigma=-{1\over\sqrt{{\rm det\,}\mathfrak{g}}}\partial_{m}(\sqrt{{\rm det\,}\mathfrak{g}}\,\mathfrak{g}^{mn}\partial_{n}\sigma)

(B.3.4)

where
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on scalar functions for the metric
\mathfrak{g}
.
To prove the theorem one shows that, for an arbitrary metric
\mathfrak{g}
, the Liouville equation has a solution for
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=-1
. The Liouville equation first arose precisely in this context. Before presenting the construction of higher genus surfaces we introduce the concept of a Fuchsian group.

B.4

Fuchsian groups

A systematic approach to the classification and explicit construction of Riemann surfaces is through the use of Fuchsian groups. A Fuchsian group is defined to be isomorphic to a discrete subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Fuchsian groups are special cases of Kleinian groups which are discrete subgroups of
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
. The elements
\gamma\not=\pm I
of a Fuchsian group
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
belong to one of the following three types,

\displaystyle\gamma\hbox{ {elliptic} }\hskip 18.06749pt

\displaystyle|{\rm tr}(\gamma)|<2

\displaystyle\gamma\hbox{ {parabolic} }\hskip 5.78172pt

\displaystyle{\rm tr}(\gamma)=\pm 2

\displaystyle\gamma\hbox{ {hyperbolic} }

\displaystyle|{\rm tr}(\gamma)|>2

(B.4.1)

Since
{\rm det\,}(\gamma)=1
,
\gamma
must be conjugate, under
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, to one of the following matrices,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda&0\cr 0&\lambda^{-1}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 72.26999pt\left(\begin{matrix}\pm 1&1\cr 0&\pm 1\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(B.4.2)

The left matrix is for elliptic type with
|\lambda|=1
and hyperbolic type with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, both with
\lambda\not=\pm 1
. The right matrix is for parabolic type.

B.4.1

Action of
\Gamma
on
{\cal H}
and
{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}

Elements
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
acts on
{\cal H}
by Möbius transformation,

\displaystyle\gamma z=\gamma(z)={az+b\over cz+d}\hskip 72.26999pt\gamma=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\cr c&d\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(B.4.3)

Since the element
\gamma=-I
leaves every point of
{\cal H}
invariant, the action is effectively by the normal subgroup
PSL(2,{\mathbb{R}})=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/\{\pm I\}
. The action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
on
{\cal H}
is transitive since an arbitrary point
z=x+iy
is the image of the point
i\in{\cal H}
, namely
z=\gamma_{z}(i)
, for a group element
\gamma_{z}
whose entries satisfy
c=0,d=a^{-1}
and
x=ab,y=a^{2}
. The isotropy group of the point
i
is the
SO(2)
subgroup of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
obtained by setting
c=-b
and
d=a
. The isotropy subgroup of an arbitrary point
z
is the conjugate of
SO(2)
by the element
\gamma_{z}
given by
\gamma_{z}\,SO(2)\,\gamma_{z}^{-1}
. As a result,
{\cal H}
may be represented by the coset space,

\displaystyle{\cal H}=SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(2)

(B.4.4)

The action of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
on
{\cal H}
extends to a transitive action on
{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
so that every point
x\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
may be expressed as
x=\gamma_{x}(\infty)
for some
\gamma_{x}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

An important role will be played by the fixed points of various elements
\gamma\in SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. The classification of the elements of
SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
into elliptic, parabolic, and hyperbolic given in (
B.4
) is equivalent to the following classification in terms of their fixed points,

elliptic element

\displaystyle\hbox{ one fixed point }z\in{\cal H},\hbox{ the other at }\bar{z}

parabolic element

\displaystyle\hbox{ one fixed point, which is in }{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}

hyperbolic element

\displaystyle\hbox{ two fixed points, which are in }{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}

(B.4.5)

In particular, the classifications of (
B.4
) and (
B.4.1
) apply to the elements of an arbitrary discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. One defines,

\displaystyle\hbox{ {elliptic point of} }\Gamma

\displaystyle\hbox{is a point }z\in{\cal H}\hbox{ such that there exists}

\displaystyle\hbox{ an elliptic }\gamma\in\Gamma\hbox{ with }\gamma(z)=z

\displaystyle\hbox{ {cusp of} }\Gamma\qquad

\displaystyle\hbox{is a point }x\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}\hbox{ such that there exists}

(B.4.6)

\displaystyle\hbox{ a parabolic }\gamma\in\Gamma\hbox{ with }\gamma(x)=x

If
z
is an elliptic point of
\Gamma
, then
\gamma(z)
is an elliptic point of
\Gamma
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. If
x
is a cusp of
\Gamma
, then
\gamma(x)
is a cusp of
\Gamma
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Proposition B.5

(a) If
z\in{\cal H}
is an elliptic point of
\Gamma
then the set
\Gamma_{z}=\{\gamma\in\Gamma\,|\,\gamma(z)=z\}
is a finite cyclic group. (b) The set of elements of finite order in
\Gamma
consist of the elliptic elements of
\Gamma
together with
\pm I
.

To prove (a) we note that
\Gamma_{z}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
. Representing
z=\gamma_{z}(i)
we see that
\Gamma_{z}
is the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma
for which
\gamma_{z}^{-1}\gamma\gamma_{z}(i)=i
. The isotropy group of the point
i
was earlier identified as
SO(2)
so that
\Gamma_{z}\subset SO(2)
. Since
\Gamma_{z}
is discrete and a subgroup of the compact group
SO(2)
,
\Gamma_{z}
must be finite. Since all finite subgroups of
SO(2)
are cyclic,
\Gamma_{z}
must also be cyclic.
To prove (b), we use the fact that an element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is conjugate under
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
to a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
\lambda,\lambda^{-1}\in{\mathbb{C}}
as in (
B.4.2
). Finite order implies that there exists a positive integer
n
such that
\lambda^{n}=1
. When
n=1,2
we have
\gamma=\pm I
while for
n>2
,
\gamma
must be elliptic.
Conversely, every elliptic element is of finite order by (a).

B.5

Construction of Riemann surfaces via Fuchsian groups

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
for an arbitrary compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
is discrete and non-Abelian. The universal (i.e. simply connected) covering space
\hat{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
may be realized as a fiber bundle over
\Sigma
with structure group isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Since
\Sigma
admits a metric
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
of constant negative curvature
R_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}}=-1
by the above theorem, the universal covering surface admits a metric of constant negative curvature as well. Therefore, by the uniformization theorem, we conclude that
\hat{\Sigma}={\cal H}
or the conformaly isomorphic
{\cal D}
. Conversely, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
must act on
\hat{\Sigma}
by an isometry of the constant curvature metric. Since we have
\hat{\Sigma}={\cal H}
, whose isometry group is
PSL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, the action of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on
{\cal H}
must be by a representation
\rho
,

\displaystyle\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\mapsto PSL(2,{\mathbb{R}})

(B.5.1)

which assigns an element of
PSL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
to each equivalence class of closed curves on
\Sigma
with a specified, though arbitrary, base point
P
. To realize these assignments concretely, we choose a base point
P\in\Sigma
, as well as a set of homology generators
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{J}
for
I,J=1,\cdots,g
passing through
P
and intersecting one another no-where else, as shown in Figure
20
.

We now cut the surface
\Sigma
along each one of the closed curves
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{J}
to unfold
\Sigma
into a polygonal simply connected domain in
{\mathbb{C}}
, as shown schematically for genus 2 in Figure
23
. To reconstruct the surface from the fundamental domain represented in Figure
23
, we pairwise identify the curves corresponding to
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{I}^{-1}
by the transformation
\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{I})
thereby closing the curves
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
. Similarly, we identify the curves
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}^{-1}
by the transformation
\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{I}^{-1})
. Clearly, the composition of all commutators must give the identity, so we must have,

\displaystyle\rho(\mathfrak{C}_{1})\rho(\mathfrak{C}_{2})\cdots\rho(\mathfrak{C}_{g})=I\hskip 72.26999pt\rho(\mathfrak{C}_{I})=\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{I})\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{I})\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{I})^{-1}\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{I})^{-1}

(B.5.2)

Each homotopy class of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
contains a unique geodesic, or closed curve of minimal length in the hyperbolic metric. Choosing the closed curves
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{J}
to be geodesics, we obtain a representation of
\Sigma
in
{\cal D}
by a domain
\Sigma_{\cal D}
whose boundary is the union of circular arcs each of which is a geodesic of
{\cal D}
, as represented in Figure
23
. The hyperbolic length of the closed curve
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
is identical to the length of
\mathfrak{A}_{I}^{-1}
and similarly the lengths of the segments
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{I}^{-1}
are equal to one another.

\mathfrak{B}_{1}
\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{-1}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
\mathfrak{A}_{2}

\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{-1})
\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{2}^{-1})

:
A fundamental domain for a compact genus-two Riemann surface
\Sigma
in the hyperbolic disc
{\cal D}
. The surface
\Sigma
is recovered by identifying the curves
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
with
\mathfrak{A}_{I}^{-1}
and identifying the curves
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
with
\mathfrak{B}_{I}^{-1}
.

B.5.1

Compactification of
\Gamma\backslash{\cal H}

We choose the standard topology of
{\cal H}
generated by open sets given by open metric discs. Consider a Fuchsian group
\Gamma\subset SL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
whose cusps form a set
{\cal C}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
. To
\Gamma
we associate the space,

\displaystyle\bar{\cal H}={\cal H}\cup{\cal C}

(B.5.3)

To specify the topology of
\bar{\cal H}
we add open sets that contain the cusps in
{\cal C}
to the topology of
{\cal H}
. For the cusp
x=\infty
and the cusps
x\not=\infty
, these open sets may be chosen as follows,

\displaystyle x=\infty

\displaystyle\{\infty\}\cup\big\{z\in{\cal H}\,\hbox{ such that }\,{\rm Im\,}(z)>y>0\hbox{ for }y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\big\}

\displaystyle x\not=\infty

\displaystyle\{x\}\cup\big\{z\in{\cal H}\,\hbox{ such that }\,|z-x-iy|<y\hbox{ for }y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\big\}

(B.5.4)

The space
\bar{\cal H}
equipped with this topology is clearly not compact. In fact, it is not even locally compact.
41
41

41

A topological space
X
is locally compact if every point in
X
has an open neighborhood which is contained in a compact subset of
X
.
The group
\Gamma
consistently acts of
\bar{\cal H}
which allows us to define the quotient,

\displaystyle X(\Gamma)=\Gamma\backslash\bar{\cal H}

(B.5.5)

The quotient space
\Gamma\backslash\bar{\cal H}
is locally compact. Finally, we state the following proposition here without proof.

Proposition B.6

If the space
\Gamma\backslash\bar{\cal H}
is compact then the number of
\Gamma
-inequivalent cusps (resp. the number of elliptic points) is finite.

]
and by Farkas and Kra
[
339
]
. Comprehensive lecture notes by Bost may be found in the collection
[
340
]
. A detailed treatment of modular curves and their compactification may be found in Chapters 2 and 3 of
[
