]
, where a proof of Proposition
B.6
is also given.

Appendix C

Line bundles on Riemann surfaces

In this appendix, we shall present a brief review of complex line bundles over Riemann surfaces. We begin by
giving a general mathematical definition of a complex line bundle over a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the topological classification of line bundles, divisors, the Riemann-Roch theorem, and various dimension formulas including the dimension of moduli space. We then discuss the same topic of line bundles from a more physics-oriented point of view as spaces of vector fields, forms, and spinors.

C.1

Holomorphic line bundles on a Riemann surface

A complex line bundle
L
on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
provides an assignment of a one-dimensional complex vector space
L_{p}
to each point
p\in\Sigma
. A local description of the line bundle
L
may be given by using a covering of
\Sigma
with open sets
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
so that
\bigcup_{\alpha}{\cal U}_{\alpha}=\Sigma
. Introducing local complex coordinates
z_{\alpha},\bar{z}_{\alpha}
in each open set makes
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
into a coordinate chart. Locally in each coordinate chart, the bundle is a direct product
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbb{C}}
and each section
f
of
L
reduces to a smooth
{\mathbb{C}}
-valued function
f_{\alpha}
on
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
. In the intersection
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\beta}
the functions
f_{\alpha}
and
f_{\beta}
are related by transition functions
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
,

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}=\varphi_{\alpha\beta}f_{\beta}\hskip 36.135pt\hbox{ in }\quad{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\beta}

(C.1.1)

The transition functions
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
are nowhere vanishing and, for a holomorphic line bundle, must be holomorphic functions. Considering now the intersection of three coordinate charts, we obtain a condition on products of transition functions,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\alpha\beta}\,\varphi_{\beta\gamma}=\varphi_{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 36.135pt\hbox{ in }\quad{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\beta}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\gamma}

(C.1.2)

Note that, for a holomorphic line bundle
L
, the transition functions are holomorphic, but the sections
f
and the
local functions
f_{\alpha}
need not be holomorphic.

C.1.1

Topological classification

To obtain the topological classification of line bundles over
\Sigma
, we solve the non-vanishing condition on the transition functions
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
in terms of exponentials,
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}=\exp(2\pi i\psi_{\alpha\beta})
, where
\psi_{\alpha\beta}
is defined by
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
up to the addition of an arbitrary integer
n_{\alpha\beta}
. The product relation (
C.1.2
) is then equivalent to the fact that the combinations,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\psi_{\alpha\beta}+\psi_{\beta\gamma}-\psi_{\alpha\gamma}

(C.1.3)

are integers since their exponential equals 1 by (
C.1.2
). The transformations
\psi_{\alpha\beta}\to\psi_{\alpha\beta}+n_{\alpha\beta}
imply transformations of
c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
by an exact cocyle
n_{\alpha\beta}+n_{\beta\gamma}-n_{\alpha\gamma}
,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\to c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+n_{\alpha\beta}+n_{\beta\gamma}-n_{\alpha\gamma}

(C.1.4)

By construction in terms of
\psi_{\alpha\beta}
, the integers
c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
satisfy the following closed cocycle relation on the intersection of four coordinate charts,

\displaystyle c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}-c_{\beta\gamma\delta}+c_{\gamma\delta\alpha}-c_{\delta\alpha\beta}=0

(C.1.5)

The space of closed cocycles
c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
satisfying (
C.1.5
) modulo exact cocycles
n_{\alpha\beta}+n_{\beta\gamma}-n_{\alpha\gamma}
is the second Čech cohomology group of
\Sigma
with integer coefficients, denoted
H^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

One may translate the Čech cohomology formulation of line bundles into the language of differential forms, de Rham cohomology, and gauge fields, which is more familiar to physicists. It will be convenient here to assume that
\Sigma
is compact so that one may extract from the covering of
\Sigma
by open sets
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
a covering with only a finite number of open sets, and avoid issues of convergence.

Each line bundle
L
on
\Sigma
corresponds to a cohomology class of
H^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})
, labeled by the first Chern class
c_{1}(L)
. In turn, the first Chern class
c_{1}(L)
may be represented in terms of a de Rham cohomology class
[F]
where
F
is the curvature 2-form of a
U(1)
connection 1-form. In each coordinate chart
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
a representative
F
of the class
[F]
may be expressed in terms of a local connection form
A_{\alpha}
by
F=dA_{\alpha}
. The transition functions for the connection are,
42
42

42

To be precise the de Rham theorem states an isomorphism between the de Rham cohomology group
H^{2}_{{\rm dR}}(\Sigma)
and the Cech cohomology group with real coefficients
H^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
.

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}-A_{\beta}=d\lambda_{\alpha\beta}

(C.1.6)

with
c_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda_{\beta\gamma}-\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}
. The first Chern class, defined by,

\displaystyle c_{1}(L)={i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}F

(C.1.7)

takes integer values, so that
H^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}
. For later use, we note that the first Chern class of the tensor product of two line bundles
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
over
\Sigma
is given as follows,

\displaystyle c_{1}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})=c_{1}(L_{1})+c_{1}(L_{2})

(C.1.8)

We conclude that the topological classification of line bundles
L
over a compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
is in terms of a single integer
d
, referred to as the degree of
L
, which labels the first Chern class
c_{1}(L)
of
L
.

C.1.2

Holomorphic classification: divisors

Holomorphic line bundles in a given topological class characterized by the degree
c_{1}(L)=d
are not necessarily equivalent holomorphically. The space of all holomorphic line bundles for given degree
d
is referred to as the Picard variety,

\displaystyle{\rm Pic}_{d}(\Sigma)=\left\{\hbox{holomorphic line bundles }L\hbox{ on }\Sigma\hbox{ with }c_{1}(L)=d\right\}

(C.1.9)

The special case of
d=0
corresponds to the Jacobian variety
J(\Sigma)={\rm Pic}_{0}(\Sigma)
.

A convenient way to characterize a holomorphic line bundle is by its
divisor
, which is a complex codimension-one subset of
\Sigma
given by a formal sum over distinct points
p_{\alpha}\in\Sigma
,

\displaystyle D=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}

(C.1.10)

The integer
n_{\alpha}
denotes the order of the point
p_{\alpha}
in the divisor
D
and may take positive or negative values, while points for which
n_{\alpha}=0
are usually omitted from the sum.

To construct the line bundle
L
associated with a divisor
D
, we introduce coordinate charts
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
with
p_{\alpha}\in{\cal U}_{\alpha}
and a local complex coordinate
z_{\alpha}
in
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
that vanishes at
p_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha=1,\cdots,N
. By choosing each
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
small enough we can make them mutually disjoint
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\beta}=\emptyset
for
\alpha\not=\beta
. We denote the open complement to all the points
p_{\alpha}
by
{\cal U}_{\infty}=\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{N}\}
so that
{\cal U}_{\infty}\bigcup_{\alpha}{\cal U}_{\alpha}=\Sigma
. The line bundle
L
corresponding to the divisor
D
is then constructed by taking transition functions in the intersections
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\infty}
to be
z_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}
, the integers
n_{\alpha}
corresponding to the order of the point
p_{\alpha}
in the divisor
D
in (
C.1.10
). A section
f
of
L
, described locally by functions
f_{\alpha}
on
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
and
f_{\infty}
in
{\cal U}_{\infty}
, satisfies
f_{\alpha}=z_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}f_{\infty}
. Conversely, given a line bundle
L
and a section
f
, the corresponding divisor may be recovered uniquely from the zeros and poles of
f
. Since the open sets
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
are mutually disjoint, all intersections with more than two
{\cal U}
coordinate charts are empty. As a result, the cocycle conditions (
C.1.2
) and (
C.1.5
) are automatically satisfied.

Adding the contributions from each transition function to the first Chern class, we find,

\displaystyle d=c_{1}(L)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}n_{\alpha}

(C.1.11)

In other words, the degree
d
of
L
is the total number of zeros (counted with their orders
n_{\alpha}
when
n_{\alpha}>0
) minus the total number of poles (counted with their orders
-n_{\alpha}
when
n_{\alpha}<0
).
A topologically trivial bundle
L
with
d=0
has an equal number of zeros and poles (counted with their orders) and its meromorphic sections are the meromorphic functions on
\Sigma
. One may introduce an equivalence relation between divisors such that
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are equivalent to one another if they differ by a divisor of degree zero. When this is the case, the ratio
f_{2}/f_{1}
of any two sections
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
respectively, has degree zero. The equivalence class with representative divisor
D
is denoted by
[D]
.

C.1.3

Examples

A familiar example in Physics is provided by the Dirac magnetic monopole, which may be thought of having a constant field strength
F
on the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
. Denoting the North and South poles by
p_{\pm}
, we introduce coordinate charts
{\cal U}_{\pm}=\hat{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{p_{\mp}\}
and local coordinates
z_{\pm}
which vanish at
p_{\pm}
. Choosing
f_{-}=1
and
f_{+}=z_{+}^{n_{+}}
gives
\psi_{+-}=n_{+}\ln(z_{+})/(2\pi i)
and
A_{+}-A_{-}=n_{+}dz_{+}/(2\pi iz_{+})
, so that
c_{1}(L)=n_{+}
for a Dirac monopole of charge
n_{+}
.

The tangent bundle
T\Sigma
over a Riemann surface
\Sigma
is a bundle of rank 2 over the reals. Using the complex structure of
\Sigma
it may be decomposed into the direct sum of its holomorphic component
T\Sigma_{(1,0)}
and its complex conjugate
T\Sigma_{(0,1)}
. The cotangent bundle
T^{*}\Sigma
may be decomposed analogously,

\displaystyle T\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\Sigma_{(1,0)}\oplus T\Sigma_{(0,1)}

\displaystyle T^{*}\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{*}\Sigma_{(1,0)}\oplus T^{*}\Sigma_{(0,1)}

(C.1.12)

The bundles
T\Sigma_{(1,0)}
and
T^{*}\Sigma_{(1,0)}
are holomorphic line bundles over
\Sigma
. The holomorphic cotangent bundle
T^{*}\Sigma_{(1,0)}
is also referred to as the canonical bundle over
\Sigma
and denoted
K
, while
T\Sigma_{(1,0)}
is isomorphic to
K^{-1}
.

This decomposition may be rendered explicit on the sections of the bundles. In terms of real local coordinates
\xi^{m}
on
\Sigma
a section of
T\Sigma
is a vector field and may be expressed as
v^{m}\partial_{m}
while a section of
T^{*}\Sigma
is a differential one-form that takes the form
\omega_{m}d\xi^{m}
. In local complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
they may be decomposed as follows,

\displaystyle v^{m}\,\partial_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{z}\,\partial_{z}+v^{\bar{z}}\,\partial_{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle\omega_{m}\,d\xi^{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{z}\,dz+\omega_{\bar{z}}\,d{\bar{z}}

(C.1.13)

where
v^{z}\,\partial_{z}
is a section of
T\Sigma_{(1,0)}
and
\omega_{z}\,dz
is a section of
T^{*}\Sigma_{(1,0)}
and similarly for their complex conjugates. Their transition functions are given as follows,

\displaystyle(v^{z})_{\alpha}(z_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(v^{z})_{\beta}(z_{\beta})\,\left(\partial z_{\alpha}/\partial z_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle(\omega_{z})_{\alpha}(z_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\omega_{z})_{\beta}(z_{\beta})\,\left(\partial z_{\alpha}/\partial z_{\beta}\right)^{-1}

(C.1.14)

where
\partial z_{\alpha}/\partial z_{\beta}
is a holomorphic nowhere vanishing transition function in
{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap\,{\cal U}_{\beta}
.

Finally, a spin bundle
S
on
\Sigma
is a holomorphic line bundle whose tensor square is isomorphic to the canonical bundle
K
,

\displaystyle S\otimes S\approx K\hskip 72.26999ptc_{1}(S)=\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}(K)

(C.1.15)

The relation between the Chern numbers follows from (
C.1.8
). By a slight abuse of notation, a spin bundle is some times denoted by
K^{1\over 2}
. For
g=0
the spin bundle is unique. However, for
g\geq 1
, the relation to the canonical bundle does not specify the spin bundle
S
uniquely. The different spin bundles are referred to as spin structures. On a surface of genus
g
, there are
2^{2g}
independent spin structures.

C.2

Holomorphic sections and the Riemann-Roch theorem

As emphasized earlier, while the transition functions of a holomorphic line bundle are holomorphic functions, the sections of a holomorphic line bundle are not generally holomorphic. The Cauchy-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}_{L}
for a holomorphic line bundle
L
acts on the sections of
L
by the ordinary Cauch-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}=d\bar{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\bar{z}_{\alpha}}
with respect to the local coordinate
z_{\alpha}
in each open set
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
. This action is covariant without the need for a connection, as may be seen by applying
\bar{\partial}
to local sections in the overlap of open sets
{\cal U}_{\alpha}
given by (
C.1.1
),

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}f_{\alpha}=\varphi_{\alpha\beta}\,\bar{\partial}f_{\beta}\hskip 43.36243pt\hbox{in}\hskip 43.36243pt{\cal U}_{\alpha}\cap{\cal U}_{\beta}

(C.2.1)

The subspace of all holomorphic sections of a holomorphic line bundle plays a special role and contains a lot of information on the line bundle. It may be defined as follows. To a holomorphic line bundle
L
we associate the vector space of its holomorphic sections,

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})=\{\hbox{holomorphic sections of }L\}

(C.2.2)

These spaces are finite-dimensional and their dimensions are related by the Riemann-Roch theorem, which will be proven, in a slightly different guise, in appendix
C.5
.

Theorem C.1

The Riemann-Roch theorem for a holomorphic line bundle
L
states

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\left(\bar{\partial}_{L}\right)-\dim{\rm Ker}\left(\bar{\partial}_{K\otimes L^{-1}}\right)=c_{1}(L)+{1\over 2}\chi(\Sigma)

(C.2.3)

where
K
is the canonical bundle,
\chi(\Sigma)
is the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
, and
\bar{\partial}_{L}
is the Cauchy Riemann operator acting on
L
.

C.3

Vanishing Theorem and dimension formulas

In this subsection, we shall state a vanishing theorem and combine its implications with the Riemann-Roch theorem to obtain general expressions for the dimensions of the spaces of homomorphic sections of line bundles. We shall specialize to the case of compact
\Sigma
and make use of the fact that the Euler number is then given in terms of the genus
g
of
\Sigma
by
\chi(\Sigma)=2-2g
.

We begin by obtaining some immediate consequences of the Riemann-Roch theorem. Since the only holomorphic functions on
\Sigma
are the constant functions, the corresponding bundle
L
is the trivial bundle with
c_{1}(L)=0
and we have
{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})={\mathbb{C}}
and
\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})=1
. Using the Riemann-Roch theorem for the trivial bundle
L
, we find

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{K})=g

(C.3.1)

For
g=0
there are no holomorphic 1-forms. For
g=1
we recover the observation, made long ago, that the space of holomorphic 1-forms is one-dimensional. For
g\geq 1
, there are
g
linearly independent holomorphic 1-forms on
\Sigma
already identified in appendix
B.1.4
. Setting
L=K
, and using the fact that
K\otimes K^{-1}
is the trivial bundle, the Riemann-Roch theorem implies
c_{1}(K)=2g-2
. For
g=1
we recover the fact, observed long ago, that the holomorphic 1-form
dz
is nowhere vanishing, while for
g\geq 2
we obtain the result that every holomorphic one-form has
2g-2
zeros (counted with multiplicities).

More generally, representing the divisor class
[L]
of an arbitrary line bundle
L
by a tensor power of the canonical bundle
[K^{n}]
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
, where it is understood that
K^{1\over 2}=S
is a spin bundle with specified spin structure, we have the following vanishing theorem:

Theorem C.2

The following holds

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})=0\hskip 28.90755pt\left\{\begin{matrix}g=0&\hbox{ and }&n>0\cr g\geq 2&\hbox{ and }&n<0\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(C.3.2)

The entry for
g=0
states the absence of holomorphic forms or spinors of degree greater than 0 on the sphere. The entry for
g\geq 2
states that there are no holomorphic vector fields on a higher genus Riemann surface. The proof of this theorem will be giving in appendix
C.6
, in a slightly different guise.

Combining the results of the vanishing theorem with the results obtained from the Riemann-Roch theorem for arbitrary divisor class
[L]=[K^{n}]
and using the relations
c_{1}(L)=c_{1}(K^{n})=nc_{1}(K)
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
we obtain for
n\not={1\over 2}
,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})=\left\{\begin{matrix}(2n-1)(g-1)&\hbox{ for }&g\geq 2&\hbox{and}&n\geq{3\over 2}&\cr g&&g\geq 0&&n=1&\cr 1&&g\geq 0&&n=0&\cr 1&&g=1&&n\not={1\over 2}&\cr 1-2n&&g=0&&n\leq-{1\over 2}\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(C.3.3)

For
n
a half-odd-integer, these dimensions are independent of the spin structure of
L
. But for
n={1\over 2}
the dimension of
{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})
depends on the spin structure of
L
. Spin structures are partitioned into even and odd depending on whether
\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})
is even or odd,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}(\bar{\partial}_{L})=\left\{\begin{matrix}1~({\rm mod~}2)&&g\geq 1&&n=\tfrac{1}{2}&\hbox{(odd spin structures)}\cr&&&&\cr 0~({\rm mod~}2)&&g\geq 1&&n=\tfrac{1}{2}&\hbox{(even spin structures)}\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(C.3.4)

The dimensions in (
C.3.4
) equal
1
and
0
respectively throughout moduli space for
g=1,2
and at generic points in moduli space for arbitrary
g\geq 3
. For
g\geq 3
, the dimensions may increase by a multiple of 2 at certain sub-varieties of moduli space, including at the locus of hyper-elliptic Riemann surfaces.

C.4

Tensors and spinors on
\Sigma

In Physics, we will deal with scalar, vector, tensor, and spinor fields on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
viewed as a two real dimensional surface equipped with a Riemannian metric,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{mn}(\xi)\,d\xi^{m}d\xi^{n}

(C.4.1)

in a system of real coordinates
\xi^{m}
. We shall now analyze complex line bundles, covariant derivatives and Laplace-Beltrami operators on line bundles in a tensorial formulation, and use it to prove the Riemann-Roch and vanishing theorems.

An arbitrary tensor field
\mathfrak{t}_{m_{1}\dots m_{n}}
of rank
n
may be decomposed into a direct sum of symmetric traceless tensors and scalars. Indeed, any anti-symmetric pair of indices may be contracted with the tensor
\sqrt{\mathfrak{g}}\,\varepsilon_{mn}
to produce a scalar, while the metric
\mathfrak{g}_{mn}
may be used to eliminate all traces. The resulting symmetric traceless tensor may be further decomposed into the eigenspaces of the complex structure
{\cal J}
. The convention
v^{z}\partial_{z}=v^{z}(dz)^{-1}
may be used to identify a form of negative weight with a vector field of opposite weight. Henceforth, we shall suppress the
z
and
\bar{z}
indices on these reduced tensors, so that
\mathfrak{t}=t_{z,\cdots z}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{t}}=t_{\bar{z}\cdots,\bar{z}}
. Thus, all tensor fields decompose into a direct sum of the following spaces,

\displaystyle T_{(n,0)}^{*}=\Big\{\mathfrak{t}(z):\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}}~\hbox{such that}~\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})(dz^{\prime})^{n}=\mathfrak{t}(z)(dz)^{n}\Big\}

(C.4.2)

The space
T_{(1,0)}^{*}
may be identified with the space of sections of the canonical bundle
K
, while the spaces
T^{*}_{(n,0)}
for
n>0
may be identified with the space of sections of the
n
-th tensor power
K^{n}
of the canonical bundle
K
. Finally, we include spinors by allowing
n
to take half-integer values which further requires specifying a spin structure.

C.4.1

Covariant derivatives and Laplace-Beltrami operators

On each space of sections
T_{(n,0)}^{*}
we define an
L^{2}(\Sigma)
inner product as follows,

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{t}_{1}|\mathfrak{t}_{2}\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\,(\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}})^{-n}\,\overline{\mathfrak{t}_{1}(z)}\,\mathfrak{t}_{2}(z)

(C.4.3)

The Cauchy-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}=(d\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}
acts covariantly on the spaces
T_{(n,0)}^{*}
, without the need for a Christoffel connection,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}:T_{(n,0)}^{*}\to T^{*}_{(n,1)}~~\hbox{with}~~\mathfrak{t}(z)(dz)^{n}\to\big(\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathfrak{t}(z)\big)(dz)^{n}d\bar{z}

(C.4.4)

It is often more convenient to use a covariant derivative which maps a space into a space of the same type. This may be achieved by the Cauchy-Riemann operator acting on
T^{*}_{(n,0)}
,

\displaystyle\nabla^{z}_{(n)}:T_{(n,0)}^{*}\to T_{(n-1,0)}^{*}\qquad\hbox{with}\qquad\mathfrak{t}(z)(dz)^{n}\to\nabla^{z}_{(n)}\mathfrak{t}(z)=\big(g^{z\bar{z}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathfrak{t}(z)\big)(dz)^{n-1}

(C.4.5)

To define the adjoint operator
-\nabla_{z}^{(n-1)}
of
\nabla^{z}_{(n)}
with respect to the
L^{2}(\Sigma)
inner product does require a Christoffel connection and is given by,

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{(n)}:T_{(n,0)}^{*}\to T_{(n+1,0)}^{*}~~\hbox{with}~~\mathfrak{t}(z)(dz)^{n}\to\big(\nabla_{z}\mathfrak{t}(z)\big)(dz)^{n+1}

(C.4.6)

The adjoint operators, and the associated Laplace-Beltrami operators, are as follows,

\displaystyle\left(\nabla^{z}_{(n)}\right)^{\dagger}=-\nabla_{z}^{(n-1)}

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}_{(n)}=-2\nabla^{z}_{(n+1)}\nabla_{z}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{z}^{(n)}\right)^{\dagger}=-\nabla^{z}_{(n+1)}

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}_{(n)}=-2\nabla_{z}^{(n-1)}\nabla^{z}_{(n)}

(C.4.7)

The Laplace-Beltrami operators both map
T_{(n,0)}\to T_{(n,0)}
. The non-zero eigenvalues and their corresponding eigenfunctions are related. To see this, we observe that,

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}_{(n)}\psi=\lambda\psi

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}_{(n+1)}\left(\nabla^{(n)}_{z}\psi\right)=\lambda\left(\nabla^{(n)}_{z}\psi\right)

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}_{(n)}\varphi=\mu\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}_{(n-1)}\left(\nabla_{(n)}^{z}\varphi\right)=\mu\left(\nabla_{(n)}^{z}\varphi\right)

(C.4.8)

When
\lambda,\mu\not=0
, these maps are invertible.
As a result, the spectra of non-vanishing eigenvalues of
\Delta^{+}_{(n)}
and
\Delta^{-}_{(n+1)}
coincide and the one-to-one map between their eigenfunctions is provided by the above relations. No such correspondence follows for the kernels of these operators. Since the Laplace-Beltrami operators are positive, their kernels are related to the kernels of the Cauchy-Riemann operators as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}\,\Delta^{+}_{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}\,\nabla_{z}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}\,\Delta^{-}_{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ker}\,\nabla^{z}_{(n)}

(C.4.9)

In the subsequent sections, we shall relate the dimensions of these kernels to one another by the Riemann-Roch and vanishing theorems.

C.5

Proof of the Riemann-Roch theorem

The kernel of
\nabla_{(n)}^{z}
is the space of holomorphic differentials of rank
n
for
n\geq 1
, and holomorphic vector fields of rank
-n
for
n\leq-1
. In this formulation, the Riemann-Roch theorem for a compact Riemann surface
\Sigma
may be stated as follows,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\,\nabla^{z}_{(n+1)}-\dim{\rm Ker}\,\nabla_{z}^{(n)}=-\left(n+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\chi(\Sigma)=(2n+1)(h-1)

(C.5.1)

where
\chi(\Sigma)=2-2h
is the Euler number of
\Sigma
. The Riemann-Roch theorem may equivalently be recast in terms of Laplace-Beltrami operators,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\,\Delta_{(n+1)}^{-}-\dim{\rm Ker}\,\Delta_{(n)}^{+}=(2n+1)(h-1)

(C.5.2)

To prove this result, we use the fact established earlier that the non-zero eigenvalues of
\Delta_{(n)}^{+}
and
\Delta_{(n+1}^{-}
are identical to obtain the following relation,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\,\Delta_{(n+1)}^{-}-\dim{\rm Ker}\,\Delta_{(n)}^{+}={\rm Tr}\left(e^{-s\Delta_{(n+1)}^{-}}\right)-{\rm Tr}\left(e^{-s\Delta_{(n)}^{+}}\right)

(C.5.3)

valid for all
0<s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In particular, the right side may be evaluated in the limit
s\to 0
where the short-time asymptotics of the heat-kernel may be used to derive an explicit formula. The short-time expansion, or Bott-Seeley expansion, is given as follows,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left(f\,e^{-s\Delta_{(n)}^{\pm}}\right)={1\over 4\pi s}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}f+{1\pm 3n\over 12\pi}\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}R_{\mathfrak{g}}f+{\cal O}(s)

(C.5.4)

Setting
f=1
, substituting the expressions into (
C.5.3
), and using the integral expression for the Euler number, we
establish (
C.5.2
), which proves the Riemann-Roch theorem.

C.6

Proof of the vanishing theorem

In terms of the differential operators
\nabla^{z}_{(n)}
and
\nabla^{z}_{(n)}
the vanishing theorem read as follows,

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\,\nabla^{z}_{(n)}=0

\displaystyle g=0\hbox{ and }n>0

\displaystyle\dim{\rm Ker}\,\nabla^{z}_{(n)}=0

\displaystyle g\geq 2\hbox{ and }n<0

(C.6.1)

The proof of these theorems uses the following equations from differential geometry,

\displaystyle{}[\nabla_{m},\nabla_{n}]\,\mathfrak{t}_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{t}_{q}\,R^{q}{}_{pmn}

\displaystyle{}[\nabla_{m},\nabla_{n}]\,\mathfrak{t}^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{t}^{q}\,R^{p}{}_{qmn}

(C.6.2)

where
R^{q}{}_{pmn}
is the Riemann tensor, and
t_{q}
is a rank one tensor, i.e. a one-form. Adapted to the case of two dimensions, and working in complex coordinates, the formulas reduce to,

\displaystyle{}[\nabla_{z},\nabla_{\bar{z}}]\,\mathfrak{t}_{z}=-\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}R_{\mathfrak{g}}\,\mathfrak{t}_{z}

(C.6.3)

where
R_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Gaussian curvature (normalized to be one for the round sphere of unit radius). Formulas for tensors and spinors
\mathfrak{t}_{z\cdots z}=\mathfrak{t}\in T_{(n,0)}^{*}
of arbitrary rank
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
may be derived by taking the tensor product and the square root, and we find,

\displaystyle{}[\nabla_{z},\nabla_{\bar{z}}]\,\mathfrak{t}=-n\,\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}\,R_{\mathfrak{g}}\,\mathfrak{t}

(C.6.4)

We are now ready to prove the vanishing theorem. We use the following rearrangement formula, obtained by integrating by parts (no boundary terms arise since
\Sigma
has no boundary),

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\,(\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}})^{n-1}|\nabla_{\bar{z}}\,\mathfrak{t}|^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}d\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}\Bigg((\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}})^{n-1}\,|\nabla_{\bar{z}}\bar{\mathfrak{t}}|^{2}+n(\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}})^{n}R_{\mathfrak{g}}\,|\mathfrak{t}|^{2}\Bigg)

(C.6.5)

\bullet
For the case
g=0
, we may choose a metric of constant positive curvature
R_{\mathfrak{g}}=1
on the sphere. Since we have
n>0
the right side of (
C.6.5
) is positive definite in
\mathfrak{t}
so that the left side cannot vanish unless
\mathfrak{t}=0
. As a result there are no holomorphic differential forms of any positive weight
n>0
on the sphere which proves the result on the first line of (
C.6
).

\bullet
For the case
g\geq 2
, we may choose a metric of constant negative curvature
R_{\mathfrak{g}}=-1
. Since we have
n<0
the right side of (
C.6.5
) is again positive definite so that the left side cannot vanish unless
\mathfrak{t}=0
. As a result there are no holomorphic vector fields of any negative weight
n<0
on a higher genus Riemann surface. In particular, this implies that there are no conformal Killing vectors or conformal Killing spinors on a Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 2
, and thus no continuous symmetries.

\bullet
For the case
g=1
, and a flat metric
R_{\mathfrak{g}}=0
on the torus, the relation (
C.6.5
) imposes no restrictions.

C.7

The dimension of moduli space

The moduli space
{\cal M}_{g}
of compact Riemann surfaces of arbitrary genus
g
is the space of inequivalent complex structures or, equivalently, the space of inequivalent conformal structures. Complex structures and conformal structures may be parametrized with the help of the Weyl-invariant tensor
{\cal J}_{m}{}^{n}=\sqrt{\mathfrak{g}}\,\varepsilon_{mp}\,\mathfrak{g}^{pn}
. Two tensors that are related by a diffeomorphsim of
\Sigma
are equivalent to one another, so that the moduli space is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{g}=\{{\cal J}\}/{\rm Diff}(\Sigma)

(C.7.1)

To compute the dimension of
{\cal M}_{g}
, we evaluate the dimension of its tangent space at a given complex structure with local complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
in terms of which the metric is given by
\mathfrak{g}=2\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}|dz|^{2}
, and the complex structure tensor
{\cal J}
has the following components,

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{z}{}^{\bar{z}}=-{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{z}=0\hskip 72.26999pt{\cal J}_{z}{}^{z}=-{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{\bar{z}}=i

(C.7.2)

Next, we compute the variation of the complex structure tensor, as the metric
\mathfrak{g}
is varied, leaving the system of complex coordinates
z,\bar{z}
unchanged. The variation of the metric is used to first order,

\displaystyle\delta\mathfrak{g}=2\delta\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}\,|dz|^{2}+\delta\mathfrak{g}_{zz}\,dz^{2}+\delta\mathfrak{g}_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}\,d\bar{z}^{2}

(C.7.3)

The effect of the infinitesimal Weyl transformation
\delta\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}
cancels out of the variation of
{\cal J}
since the complex structure is invariant under Weyl transformations. Thus, we obtain the following variations, to first order in
\delta\mathfrak{g}_{zz}
and
\delta\mathfrak{g}_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}
,

\displaystyle\delta{\cal J}_{z}{}^{z}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta{\cal J}_{z}{}^{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=+i\,\mathfrak{g}_{z\bar{z}}\,\delta\mathfrak{g}^{\bar{z}\bar{z}}~~\in~T^{*}_{(1,-1)}

\displaystyle\delta{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{z}

\displaystyle=-i\,\mathfrak{g}_{\bar{z}z}\,\delta\mathfrak{g}^{zz}~~\in~T^{*}_{(-1,1)}

(C.7.4)

Moduli deformations, however, are variations of complex structure modulo diffeomorphisms. The action of an infinitesimal diffeomorphism
v^{m}\partial_{m}
on the metric is given as follows,

\displaystyle\delta_{v}\mathfrak{g}_{mn}=\nabla_{m}v_{n}+\nabla_{n}v_{m}

(C.7.5)

Under this variation, the complex structure transforms as follows,

\displaystyle\delta_{v}\,{\cal J}_{z}{}^{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\,\nabla_{z}^{(1)}v^{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle\delta_{v}\,{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+i\,\nabla_{\bar{z}}^{(-1)}v^{z}

(C.7.6)

Vectors in the tangent space
T_{\mathfrak{g}}{\cal M}_{g}
to moduli space
{\cal M}_{g}
at a given metric
\mathfrak{g}
, may be identified with complex structure deformations that are in the orthogonal complement to the ranges of these operators, evaluated at the metric
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{g}}{\cal M}_{g}=\Big({\rm Range}~\nabla_{z}^{(1)}\Big)^{\dagger}\oplus\Big({\rm Range}~\nabla_{\bar{z}}^{(-1)}\Big)^{\dagger}

(C.7.7)

But the orthogonal complement to the range of any operator is the kernel of the adjoint of that operator.
Since we have
(\nabla_{z}^{(1)})^{\dagger}=-\nabla^{z}_{(2)}
, we find,

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{g}}{\cal M}_{g}={\rm Ker}\nabla^{z}_{(2)}\oplus{\rm Ker}\nabla^{\bar{z}}_{(-2)}

(C.7.8)

First of all, we note that the tangent space to
{\cal M}_{g}
is split according to holomorphic and anti-holomorphic directions, so that
{\cal M}_{g}
is a complex manifold (actually, more precisely a complex orbifold). The dimension of moduli space may be read off from the table of dimensions given in the preceding subsection,

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\cal M}_{g}=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&g=0\cr 1&g=1\cr 3g-3&g\geq 2\cr\end{matrix}\right.

(C.7.9)

The combination
\delta{\cal J}_{\bar{z}}{}^{z}
and its complex conjugate are often referred to as Beltrami differentials. The elements of the dual space
{\rm Ker}\nabla^{z}_{(2)}
are the holomorphic quadratic differentials.

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

A summary of the ingredients of holomorphic line bundles on compact Riemann surfaces, accessible to physicists, was given in the review paper on string perturbation theory
[
190
]
and in the lecture notes
[
340
]
. A fundamental and useful reference is the book by Fay
[
341
]
. Helpful discussions of line bundles characterized by divisors may be found in the book by Farkas and Kra
[
339
]
, while the deformation theory of complex structures for Riemann surfaces is treated quite explicitly in the book by Schiffer and Spencer
[
342
]
. More general treatments of vector bundles on complex and Kähler manifolds may be found in the books by Gunning
[
343
]
and Kodaira
[
344
]
. The Bott-Seeley expansion in the context of two-dimensional Riemannian manifolds is discussed and proven in
[
345
]
and
[
190
]
.

Appendix D

Higher genus
\vartheta
-functions and meromorphic forms

In this final appendix, we shall review the modular geometry of the Siegel half-space at higher rank, Riemann
\vartheta
-functions of higher rank, the embedding of higher-genus Riemann surfaces into the Jacobian variety via the Abel map, and use these ingredients to construct the prime form, the Szegö kernel, and other meromorphic differential forms on higher-genus Riemann surfaces.

D.1

The Siegel half space

The rank
g
Siegel half space
{\cal H}_{g}
may be defined as the space of complex
g\times g
symmetric matrices with positive definite imaginary part,

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{g}=\Big\{\Omega\in{\mathbb{C}}^{g\times g},~\Omega^{t}=\Omega,~Y={\rm Im\,}(\Omega)>0\Big\}

(D.1.1)

Its dimension is
\dim{\cal H}_{g}={1\over 2}g(g+1)
. Alternatively,
{\cal H}_{g}
is given as the coset space

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{g}=Sp(2g,{\mathbb{R}})/U(g)

(D.1.2)

The group
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{R}})
acts on
\Omega
by

\displaystyle\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}=(A\Omega+B)(C\Omega+D)^{-1}

(D.1.3)

where the
g\times g
real matrices
A,B,C,D
are given in terms of
M\in Sp(2g,{\mathbb{R}})
by,

\displaystyle M=\left(\begin{matrix}A&B\cr C&D\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 57.81621ptM^{t}\mathfrak{J}M=\mathfrak{J}\hskip 57.81621pt\mathfrak{J}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&-I\cr I&0\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(D.1.4)

The unitary subgroup
U(g)
is generated by setting
C=-B
and
D=A
subject to the conditions
A^{t}A+B^{t}B=I
and
A^{t}B-B^{t}A=0
. Since the group
U(g)
contains a
U(1)
factor,
{\cal H}_{g}
is a Kähler manifold, with the following
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant Kähler metric,

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{g}=\sum_{I,J,K,L=1}^{g}(Y^{-1})^{IJ}\,d\Omega_{IK}\,(Y^{-1})^{KL}\,d\bar{\Omega}_{JL}

(D.1.5)

The associated Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta
on scalar functions of
{\cal H}_{g}
is given by,

\displaystyle\Delta=\sum_{I,J,K,L=1}^{g}4\,Y_{IJ}Y_{KL}\,\partial^{IK}\,\bar{\partial}^{JL}

(D.1.6)

where the derivatives with respect to the components of
\Omega_{IJ}
are defined as follows,

\displaystyle\partial^{II}={\partial\over\partial\Omega_{II}}\hskip 72.26999pt\partial^{IJ}={1\over 2}{\partial\over\partial\Omega_{IJ}}\qquad\hbox{ for }J\not=I

(D.1.7)

For genus one,
{\cal H}_{1}={\cal H}
coincides with the Poincaré upper half-plane,
ds_{1}^{2}
with the Poincaré metric on
{\cal H}_{1}
, and
\Delta
with the Laplace-Beltrami operator on scalars functions on
{\cal H}_{1}
.

D.2

The Riemann theta function

The Riemann
\vartheta
-functions are complex-valued functions on
{\cal H}_{g}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{g}
that generalize the Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions on
{\cal H}\times{\mathbb{C}}
to arbitrary rank
g
. Just as for Jacobi
\vartheta
-functions, Riemann
\vartheta
-functions may be considered for arbitrary complex characteristics
\delta=[\delta^{\prime}|\delta^{\prime\prime}]
with
\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2g}
. In terms of local coordinates
\Omega
on
{\cal H}_{g}
and
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{g})^{t}
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{g}
, the Riemann
\vartheta
-function for characteristic
\delta
may be defined by the following absolutely convergent series,

\displaystyle\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)\equiv\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{g}}\exp\biggl(i\pi(n+\delta^{\prime})^{t}\Omega(n+\delta^{\prime})+2\pi i(n+\delta^{\prime})^{t}(\zeta+\delta^{\prime\prime})\biggl)\,.

(D.2.1)

Upon shifting by
m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{g}
we have the periodicity relations,

\displaystyle\vartheta[\delta](\zeta+m+\Omega n|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\pi n^{t}\Omega n-2\pi in^{t}(\zeta+\delta^{\prime})+2\pi im^{t}\delta^{\prime\prime}}\,\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)

\displaystyle\vartheta[\delta^{\prime}+n|\delta^{\prime\prime}+m](\zeta|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\pi im^{t}\delta^{\prime}}\,\vartheta[\delta^{\prime}|\delta^{\prime\prime}](\zeta|\Omega)

(D.2.2)

The
\vartheta
-function without characteristics is often denoted by
\vartheta(\zeta|\Omega)=\vartheta[0](\zeta|\Omega)
.

We shall focus on half-integer characteristics
\delta
with values
\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{g}
corresponding to a spin structure. The parity
4\delta^{\prime}\cdot\delta^{\prime\prime}
(mod 2) of a
\vartheta
-function with half-integer characteristics depends on
\delta
and, at a point
(\zeta|\Omega)
where
\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)\not=0
, is defined by,

\displaystyle\vartheta[\delta](-\zeta|\Omega)=(-1)^{4\delta^{\prime}\cdot\delta^{\prime\prime}}\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)

(D.2.3)

The spin structure
\delta
is referred to as an even or odd spin structure according to whether the integer
4\delta^{\prime}\cdot\delta^{\prime\prime}
is even or odd.

D.2.1

Modular transformations

The discrete (or arithmetic) subgroup
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
of
Sp(2g,{\mathbb{R}})
is the group of modular transformations, whose action on the Siegel upper half-space
{\cal H}_{g}
is given by (
D.1.3
), but now with
M\in Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
with the entries of the matrices
A,B,C,D
being integers. Under a modular transformation
M\in Sp(2g,{\mathbb{Z}})
, a half-integer characteristic
\delta=[\delta^{\prime}|\,\delta^{\prime\prime}]
transforms into another half-integer characteristic
\tilde{\delta}=[\tilde{\delta}^{\prime}|\,\tilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime}]
given as follows,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{\delta}^{\prime}\cr\tilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}D&-C\cr-B&A\cr\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\delta^{\prime}\cr\delta^{\prime\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)+{1\over 2}\,{\rm diag}\left(\begin{matrix}CD^{t}\cr AB^{t}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\hskip 50.58878ptM=\left(\begin{matrix}A&B\cr C&D\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(D.2.4)

Under joint modular transformations of
\Omega
and
\delta
, the
\vartheta
-function transforms as follows,

\displaystyle\vartheta[\tilde{\delta}]\left(\{(C\Omega+D)^{-1}\}^{t}\zeta|\tilde{\Omega}\right)=\varepsilon(\delta,M)\,{\rm det\,}(C\Omega+D)^{{1\over 2}}\,\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)

(D.2.5)

where
\varepsilon(\delta,M)^{8}=1
and the precise dependence of
\varepsilon(\delta,M)
on its arguments is given in
[
341
,
346
]
.

D.2.2

Riemann relations on
\vartheta
-functions

For each spin structure
\lambda
, there exists a Riemann relation which may be expressed as a quadrilinear sum over all spin structures
\kappa
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa}\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{1})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{2})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{3})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{4})=4\,\vartheta[\lambda](\zeta_{1}^{\prime})\vartheta[\lambda](\zeta_{2}^{\prime})\vartheta[\lambda](\zeta_{3}^{\prime})\vartheta[\lambda](\zeta_{4}^{\prime})

(D.2.6)

The signature symbol for the pairing of two spin structures is defined by,

\displaystyle\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle=e^{4\pi i(\kappa^{\prime}\cdot\lambda^{\prime\prime}-\kappa^{\prime\prime}\cdot\lambda^{\prime})}

(D.2.7)

and the relation between the vectors
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
is given in terms of a matrix
\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta_{1}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{2}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{3}^{\prime}\cr\zeta_{4}^{\prime}\cr\end{matrix}\right)=\Lambda\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta_{1}\cr\zeta_{2}\cr\zeta_{3}\cr\zeta_{4}\cr\end{matrix}\right)\qquad\qquad\Lambda={1\over 2}\left(\begin{matrix}1&1&1&1\cr 1&1&-1&-1\cr 1&-1&1&-1\cr 1&-1&-1&1\cr\end{matrix}\right)

(D.2.8)

which satisfies
\Lambda^{2}=I
. In the special case where
\zeta=\zeta^{\prime}=0
, only even spin structures
\kappa=\delta
contribute to the sum and we recover a Riemann identity for each odd spin structure
\lambda=\nu
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta}\langle\nu|\delta\rangle\vartheta[\delta]^{4}(0,\Omega)=0

(D.2.9)

More generally, if
\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}+\zeta_{3}+\zeta_{4}=0
then
\zeta_{1}^{\prime}=0
, the right side of (
D.2.6
) vanishes for any odd spin structure
\lambda
, and the Riemann relations become,

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa}\langle\kappa|\lambda\rangle\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{1})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{2})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{3})\vartheta[\kappa](\zeta_{4})=0

(D.2.10)

For genus one, there is only a single odd spin structure and the Riemann relation reduces to the famous Jacobi identity
\vartheta_{2}^{4}-\vartheta_{3}^{4}+\vartheta_{4}^{4}=0
.

D.3

The Jacobian, Abel map, and Riemann vanishing theorem

We recall from appendix
B
that a canonical basis may be chosen for the holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms
\omega_{I}
with
I=1,\cdots,g
on a compact Riemann surface of genus
g
by normalizing the integrals of
\omega_{I}
on the
\mathfrak{A}
-cycles of a canonical basis
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
of generators for the homology group
H_{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{Z}})
. The integrals on the
\mathfrak{B}
-cycles then give the period matrix
\Omega
of the Riemann surface
\Sigma
in the homology basis
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})
,

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}\omega_{J}=\delta_{IJ}\hskip 72.26999pt\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}\omega_{J}=\Omega_{IJ}

(D.3.1)

Under a symplectic change of canonical homology basis, the holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms
\omega
and period matrix
\Omega
transform as given in (
B.1.4
). In view of the Riemann relations, we have
\Omega^{t}=\Omega
and
{\rm Im\,}(\Omega)>0
, so that the period matrix of a compact Riemann surface takes values in the Siegel half space
{\cal H}_{g}
. The set of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
periods defines a lattice
\Lambda={\mathbb{Z}}^{g}+\Omega{\mathbb{Z}}^{g}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{g}
, whose quotient is an Abelian variety referred to as the the Jacobian variety,

\displaystyle J(\Sigma)={\mathbb{C}}^{g}/\{{\mathbb{Z}}^{g}+\Omega{\mathbb{Z}}^{g}\}

(D.3.2)

In other words, the Jacobian is a
g
-dimensional complex torus. For genus
g=1
, this torus gives an equivalent representation of the Riemann surface itself. For higher genus
g\geq 2
, this simple correspondence no longer holds, but is replaced by a more subtle identification produced by the Abel map.

Given a base point
z_{0}\in\Sigma
, the Abel map sends a divisor
D
of
n
points
z_{i}\in\Sigma
with weights
q_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for
i=1,\cdots,n
, formally denoted by
D=q_{1}z_{1}+\cdots q_{n}z_{n}
, into
{\mathbb{C}}^{g}
by,

\displaystyle D=q_{1}z_{1}+\cdots+q_{n}z_{n}\to\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\int_{z_{0}}^{z_{i}}(\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{g})

(D.3.3)

where the
g
-tuple
(\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{g})
stands for the vector of holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms
\omega_{I}
.
The Abel map into
{\mathbb{C}}^{g}
is multiple valued because each integral
\int_{z_{0}}^{z_{i}}\omega_{I}
is multiple-valued in
{\mathbb{C}}
, but the Abel map is single valued as a map into the Jacobian
J(\Sigma)
.

Theorem D.1

The Riemann vanishing theorem states that
\vartheta(\zeta|\Omega)=0
if and only if there exist
g-1
points
p_{1},\cdots,p_{g-1}\in\Sigma
such that,

\displaystyle\zeta=p_{1}+\cdots p_{g-1}-\Delta(z_{0})\hskip 72.26999pt\zeta_{I}=\sum_{i=1}^{g-1}\int_{z_{0}}^{z_{i}}\omega_{I}-\Delta_{I}(z_{0})

(D.3.4)

where
\Delta_{I}(z_{0})
are the components of the Riemann vector for base-point
z_{0}
, given by,

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}(z_{0})=-{1\over 2}-{1\over 2}\Omega_{II}+\sum_{J\not=I}\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{J}}\omega_{J}(z)\int^{z}_{z_{0}}\omega_{I}

(D.3.5)

The combination
\zeta
is independent of the base-point.

The proof of the Riemann vanishing theorem may be found in any book on
\vartheta
-functions, or in the physics literature in
[
190
]
. For the special case of genus
g=1
, the set of points
p_{i}
is empty, and the Riemann vector reduces to
\Delta=-{1\over 2}-{1\over 2}\tau
which merely states that
\vartheta(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\tau|\tau)=0
, a fact we established long ago.

An important application of the Riemann vanishing theorem is to the existence of the following holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms, defined for any odd spin structure
\nu
by

\displaystyle\omega_{\nu}(z)=\sum_{I}\omega_{I}(z)\partial^{I}\vartheta[\nu](0|\Omega)

(D.3.6)

Its
2(h-1)
zeros are all double zeros at points
p_{i}
such that
\zeta_{I}=\nu_{I}
in (
D.3.4
). Therefore,
\omega_{\nu}(z)
admits a holomorphic square root
h_{\nu}(z)
. Since the square of
h_{\nu}
is
\omega_{\nu}(z)
, which is a section of the canonical bundle,
h_{\nu}
is a section of a spin bundle with spin structure
\nu
. For each odd spin structure
\nu
, the holomorphic
({1\over 2},0)
-form
h_{\nu}
is unique up to a sign.

D.4

The prime form

To construct the prime form we consider an arbitrary odd spin structure
\nu
. Its associated holomorphic section
h_{\nu}(z)
of the spin bundle with spin structure
\nu
has
g-1
zeros. The prime form is a
(-\tfrac{1}{2},0)
-form in both variables
z
and
w
, defined by,

\displaystyle E(z,w|\Omega)={\vartheta[\nu](z-w|\Omega)\over h_{\nu}(z)h_{\nu}(w)}

(D.4.1)

The argument
z-w
of the
\vartheta
-function stands for the Abel map of (
D.3.3
) with divisor
D=z-w
. The form
E(z,w|\Omega)
defined this way is independent of
\nu
. It is holomorphic in
z
and
w
and has a unique simple zero at
z=w
. It is single valued when
z
is moved around
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
cycles, but has non-trivial monodromy when
z\to z+\mathfrak{B}_{I}
is moved around
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
cycles,

\displaystyle E(z+\mathfrak{A}_{I},w|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(z,w|\Omega)

\displaystyle E(z+\mathfrak{B}_{I},w|\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\exp\biggl(-i\pi\Omega_{II}+2\pi i\int^{z}_{w}\!\omega_{I}\biggr)E(z,w|\Omega)

(D.4.2)

Therefore, it is properly defined on the simply connected covering space of
\Sigma\times\Sigma
, or it may be defined in a fundamental domain for
\Sigma
in the complex plane.

D.5

Holomorphic differentials

Combining the Riemann-Roch and vanishing theorems, the dimension of the space of holomorphic
(n,0)
-forms is denoted by
\Upsilon(n)=\dim{\rm Ker}\,\partial_{\bar{z}}^{(n)}
and is given by,

\displaystyle\Upsilon(n)=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&n<0\,,\ {\rm and}\ n=1/2\ {\rm even\ spin\ structure}\cr 1&n=0\,,\ {\rm and}\ n=1/2\ {\rm odd\ spin\ structure}\cr g&n=1\cr(2n-1)(g-1)&n\geq 3/2,\ {\rm and}\,g\geq 2\end{matrix}\right.

(D.5.1)

The dimensions listed for
n=1/2
are for generic moduli and are valid for exceptional moduli mod 2. A basis of holomorphic differentials is denoted by
\phi_{a}^{(n)}
,
a=1,\cdots,\Upsilon(n)
. They are holomorphic sections of the line bundles
K^{n}
, the
n
-th power of the canonical bundle
K
, for which the number of zeros is given by,

\displaystyle\#\ {\rm zeros}\ \phi^{(n)}_{a}=c_{1}(K^{n})=2n(g-1)

(D.5.2)

For a given
a
, there are
\Upsilon(n)-1
zeros fixed to be
z_{b}
with
b\not=a
, and another
g
zeros due to the Riemann vanishing theorem, giving a total of
2n(g-1)
, in agreement with the value of the first Chern class.
For
n=0
, they are constants, for
n=1/2
and
\nu
odd they are denoted by
h_{\nu}(z)
, while for
n=1
they are the holomorphic Abelian differentials
\omega_{I}
, for
I=1,\cdots,g
.

Given any set of
\Upsilon(n)
points
z_{1},\cdots,z_{\Upsilon(n)}
on the surface, we may choose a basis
\hat{\phi}^{(n)}_{a}
for the holomorphic
n
-differentials normalized at the points
z_{b}
by,

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}(z_{b})=\delta_{a}^{b}

(D.5.3)

The holomorphic differentials with this normalization may be exhibited explicitly in terms of the prime form
E(z,w)
, the
h/2
differential
\sigma(z)
and
\vartheta
-functions. For
n\geq 3/2
, we have,

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}(z)={\vartheta[\delta](z-z_{a}+\sum z_{b}-(2n-1)\Delta)\over\vartheta[\delta](\sum z_{b}-(2n-1)\Delta)}{\prod_{b\not=a}E(z,z_{b})\over\prod_{b\not=a}E(z_{a},z_{b})}\biggl({\sigma(z)\over\sigma(z_{a})}\biggr)^{2n-1}

(D.5.4)

Here,
\sigma(z)
is a holomorphic
(\tfrac{h}{2},0)
-form without zeros or poles defined, up to a constant, by the following ratio,

\displaystyle{\sigma(z)\over\sigma(w)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\vartheta(z-\sum p_{i}+\Delta)\over\vartheta(w-\sum p_{i}+\Delta)}\prod_{i=1}^{h}{E(w,p_{i})\over E(z,p_{i})}

(D.5.5)

where
p_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,h
are arbitrary points on the surface. Note that
\sigma(z)
is single valued around
\mathfrak{A}_{I}
cycles but multivalued around
\mathfrak{B}_{I}
cycles in the following way

\displaystyle\sigma(z+\mathfrak{B}_{I})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(z)\exp\bigl\{-i\pi(h-1)\Omega_{II}+2\pi i\Delta_{Iz}\bigr\}

(D.5.6)

Besides the
\Upsilon(n)-1
zeros
z_{b}
,
b\not=a
, the differential
\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}(z)
has
h
additional zeros. The differential
\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}(z)
is an
(n,0)
-form in
z
, a
(-n,0)
-form in
z_{a}
and a
(0,0)
-form
z_{b}
with
b\not=a
. For
n=1
, we have,

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}^{(1)}_{a}(z)={\vartheta(z-z_{a}+\sum z_{b}-w_{0}-\Delta)\over\vartheta(\sum z_{b}-w_{0}-\Delta)\ E(z,w)}\ \prod_{b\not=a}{E(z,z_{b})\over E(z_{a},z_{b})}{E(z_{a},w_{0})\over E(z,w_{0})}\ {\sigma(z)\over\sigma(z_{a})}\,.

(D.5.7)

D.6

Meromorphic differentials

Meromorphic differentials play the role of Green functions for the Cauchy-Riemann operators acting on holomorphic line bundles. The Green function
G_{n}(z,w)=G_{n}(z,w;z_{1},\cdots,z_{\Upsilon(n)})
for the operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}^{(n)}
is a meromorphic
(n,0)
form in
z
and
(1-n,0)
-form in
w
. When
n\geq 3/2
for general spin structure
\delta
and
n=1/2
for even spin structure
\delta
, the Green function
G_{n}
may be defined by the following relations,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}^{(n)}G_{n}(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+2\pi\delta(z,w)

(D.6.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{w}}^{(1-n)}G_{n}(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi\delta(z,w)+2\pi\sum_{a=1}^{\Upsilon(n)}\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}(z)\delta(w,z_{a})

(D.6.2)

The properly normalized holomorphic
n
-differentials
\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(n)}
are defined in (
D.5.3
) and (
D.5.4
). Setting
z=z_{a}
, we have
\partial_{\bar{w}}^{(1-n)}G_{n}(z_{a},w)=0
, so that
G_{n}(z_{a},w)=0
.
Explicit expressions for the Green’s function are

\displaystyle G_{n}(z,w)={\vartheta[\delta]\big(z-w+\sum z_{b}-(2n-1)\Delta\big)\over\vartheta[\delta]\big(\sum z_{b}-(2n-1)\Delta\big)\ E(z,w)}\ {\prod_{a}E(z,z_{a})\over\prod_{a}E(w,z_{a})}\ \biggl({\sigma(z)\over\sigma(w)}\biggr)^{2n-1}

(D.6.3)

For
n=1/2
, this reduces to the standard form of the Szegö kernel, usually denoted by

\displaystyle S_{\delta}(z,w)={\vartheta[\delta]\big(z-w\big)\over\vartheta[\delta]\big(0\big)\ E(z,w)}

(D.6.4)

For
n=1
, the Green’s function
G_{1}(z,w)=G_{1}(z,w;z_{1},\cdots,z_{h},w_{0})

is the Abelian differential of the third kind, satisfying

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}^{(1)}G_{1}(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+2\pi\delta(z,w)-2\pi\delta(z,w_{0})

(D.6.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{w}}^{(0)}G_{1}(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi\delta(z,w)+2\pi\sum_{a=1}^{h}\hat{\phi}_{a}^{(1)}(z)\ \delta(w,z_{a})

(D.6.6)

and explicitly given by the following expression

\displaystyle G_{1}(z,w)={\vartheta\big(z-w-w_{0}+\sum z_{b}-\Delta\big)\over\vartheta\big(-w_{0}+\sum z_{b}-\Delta\big)\ E(z,w)}\ {\prod_{a}E(z,z_{a})E(w,w_{0})\over\prod_{a}E(w,z_{a})E(z,w_{0})}\ {\sigma(z)\over\sigma(w)}\,.

(D.6.7)

The combination
\partial_{z}\partial_{w}\ln E(z,w)
is a meromorphic differential (Abelian of the second kind) with a single double pole at

z=w
. Its integrals around homology cycles are given by

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{A}_{I}}\!dz\partial_{z}\partial_{w}\ln E(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathfrak{B}_{I}}\!dz\partial_{z}\partial_{w}\ln E(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi i\omega_{I}(w)

(D.6.8)

and will be of use throughout.

D.7

The
bc
system

A useful unification of the formulas for holomorphic and meromorphic forms is provided by the conformal field theory correlators of anti-commuting fields
b
and
c
of weights
(n,0)
and
(1-n,0)
. This quantum field theory was already introduced in section
5.2
for a Riemann surface of genus 1, namely a torus. Here, we shall generalize the correlators to arbitrary genus
g\geq 2
. Since the roles of
b
and
c
are swapped by letting
n\to 1-n
, we shall restrict to considering the cases
n\geq\tfrac{1}{2}
without loss of generality.

As in the case of the torus, the fields
b,c
are locally holomorphic,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}b=\partial_{\bar{z}}c=0

(D.7.1)

However, as
b
and
c
fields approach one another poles may develop. The poles may be represented schematically by the singular contribution to the operator product expansion, given by a simple pole with unit residue,

\displaystyle b(z)\,c(w)\sim{1\over z-w}

(D.7.2)

All poles must arise from these operator coincidences. In view of the anti-commuting nature of the fields, the operator product of two like fields produces a simple zero,

\displaystyle b(z_{1})b(z_{2})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle(z_{1}-z_{2})\,\partial b(z_{2})\ b(z_{2})

\displaystyle c(w_{1})c(w_{2})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle(w_{1}-w_{2})\,\partial c(w_{2})\,c(w_{2})

(D.7.3)

In the case of the torus we used Fourier analysis to solve for the fields and their correlators. Since higher-genus Riemann surfaces have no continuous isometries, such as translation symmetry, Fourier analysis is no longer applicable.

Now consider the correlator of an arbitrary number of the
b
and
c
fields of weight
(n,0)
and
(1-n,0)
respectively,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{N})\,c(w_{1})\cdots c(w_{M})\rangle

(D.7.4)

Viewed as a function of
z_{1}
, the correlator has simple zeros at
z_{2},\cdots,z_{N}
and simple poles at
w_{1},\cdots,w_{M}
. But since all poles arise from operator coincidences, and no other poles can appear, the number of poles in
z_{1}
is exactly
M
, and thus the
n
-form must have
M+2n(g-1)
zeros. Now
N-1
of these zeros are specified by the positions of the points
z_{2},\cdots,z_{N}
, leaving an extra
g
zeros due to the Riemann vanishing theorem, for a total of
N-1+g
zeros. Equating the number of zeros computed in these two different ways gives,

\displaystyle N=M+(2n-1)(g-1)

(D.7.5)

The correlator must vanish unless the number of
b
and
c
fields satisfies this relation.

D.7.1

The case
n=\tfrac{1}{2}
with even spin structure

In the case
n={1\over 2}
with even spin structure
\delta
and generic moduli, neither the
b
nor the
c
field has zero modes.
By using Wick contractions, the correlator is given by,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{M})\,c(w_{M})\cdots c(w_{1})\rangle={\rm det\,}S_{\delta}(z_{i},w_{j})

(D.7.6)

where
S_{\delta}
is the Szegö kernel for even spin structure
\delta
, the determinant is taken of the
M\times M
matrix
S(z_{i},w_{j})
, and the numbering of the points
w_{j}
has been chosen in descending order so that the sign multiplying the determinant on the right side is positive. An alternative formula is obtained by matching poles and zeros in each variable, and we obtain,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{M})\,c(w_{M})\cdots c(w_{1})\rangle={\vartheta[\delta](D|\Omega)\over\vartheta[\delta](0|\Omega)}{\prod_{i<j}E(z_{i},z_{j})E(w_{i},w_{j})\over\prod_{i,j}E(z_{i},w_{j})}

(D.7.7)

where the divisor is
D=(z_{1}-w_{1})+\cdots+(z_{M}-w_{M})
. This formula is often referred to as the bosonized correlator as it may be obtained by representing the spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
fermion fields
b,c
in terms of the chiral half of a complex scalar field.

D.7.2

The case
n=\tfrac{1}{2}
with odd spin structure

In the case
n={1\over 2}
with odd spin structure
\nu
and generic moduli, there is a single zero mode
h_{\nu}
of the Dirac equation. Clearly, the two-point correlator
\langle b(z)c(w)\rangle
must be saturated by the zero modes and be proportional to
h_{\nu}(z)h_{\nu}(w)
. For higher point functions, we need a Green function. The Green function may be defined by an equation that is orthogonal to the Dirac zero mode. Such an equation is not unique, but different definitions will produce the same correlator. Preserving meromorphicity, we choose the following definition,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}S_{\nu}(z,w)=2\pi\delta(z,w)-2\pi\delta(z,w_{0}){h_{\nu}(w)\over h_{\nu}(w_{0})}

(D.7.8)

which is orthogonal to
h_{\nu}(z)
by construction. The general correlator is then given by,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{M})\,c(w_{M})\cdots c(w_{1})\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}(-)^{i+j}\langle b(z_{i})c(w_{j})\rangle\,{\rm det\,}S_{\nu}(z_{k},z_{l})\Big|_{{k\not=i,\atop l\not=j}}

(D.7.9)

The constant of proportionality in
\langle b(z)c(w)\rangle\sim h_{\nu}(z)h_{\nu}(w)
is given by the functional determinant of the Dirac operator on
n={1\over 2}
sections with odd spin structure
\nu
.

D.7.3

The case
n=1

In any non-vanishing correlator for
n=1
, the
b
field has
g
zero modes, while the
c
field has one zero mode, namely the constants. We shall normalize the correlator so that the simplest non-zero amplitude is given by,

\displaystyle{\cal G}_{0}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{g};w)=\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{g})c(w)\rangle={\rm det\,}\omega_{I}(z_{i})

(D.7.10)

where the determinant is taken of the
g\times g
matrix with entries
\omega_{I}(z_{i})
for
i,I=1,\cdots,g
. The correlator with an arbitrary number of
b,c
fields subject to the condition (
D.7.5
),

\displaystyle{\cal G}_{M}(z_{1},\cdots w_{M+1})=\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{g+M})\,c(w_{1})\cdots c(w_{M+1})\rangle

(D.7.11)

with this normalization is given by

\displaystyle{\cal G}_{M}(z_{1},\cdots,w_{M+1})={\vartheta\left(D\right)\over Z^{3}}\,{\prod_{i<j}E(z_{i},z_{j})\prod_{a<b}E(w_{a},w_{b})\prod_{i}\sigma(z_{i})\over\prod_{i,a}E(z_{i},w_{a})\prod_{a}\sigma(w_{a})}\quad

(D.7.12)

where
i,j=1,\cdots,g+M
and
a,b=1,\cdots,M+1
and
D
is defined by

\displaystyle D=\sum_{i=1}^{g+M}z_{i}-\sum_{a=1}^{M+1}w_{a}-\Delta

(D.7.13)

The factor
Z
is the chiral boson partition function determined by setting
M=0
,

\displaystyle{\rm det\,}\omega_{I}(p_{i})={\vartheta\left(p_{1}+\cdots p_{g}-q-\Delta\right)\over Z^{3}}\,{\prod_{i<j}E(p_{i},p_{j})\prod_{i}\sigma(p_{i})\over\prod_{i}E(p_{i},q)\sigma(q)}

(D.7.14)

for arbitrary points
q
and
p_{i}
with
i=1,\cdots,g
.
With this normalization the correlator transforms under modular transformations by,

\displaystyle\tilde{\cal G}_{M}(z_{1},\cdots,w_{M+1})={\rm det\,}(C\Omega+D)^{-1}{\cal G}_{M}(z_{1},\cdots,w_{M+1})

(D.7.15)

D.7.4

The case
n\geq\tfrac{3}{2}

For
n\geq\tfrac{3}{2}
, the
b
field has
\Upsilon=(2n-1)(g-1)
zero modes, while the
c
field has no zero modes.
We have the following bosonization formula,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{M+\Upsilon})\,c(w_{1})\cdots c(w_{M})\rangle={\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)\over Z}{\prod_{a<b}E(z_{a},z_{b})\prod_{i<j}E(w_{i},w_{j})\prod_{a}\sigma(z_{a})\over\prod_{i,a}E(z_{a},w_{i})\prod_{i}\sigma(w_{i})}

where
a,b=1,\cdots,M+\Upsilon
, while
i,j=1,\cdots,M
and
\zeta
is given by,

\displaystyle\zeta=\sum_{a=1}^{M+\Upsilon}z_{a}-\sum_{i=1}^{M}w_{i}-(2n-1)\Delta

(D.7.17)

In the special case where
M=0
, we recover a purely holomorphic correlator, which is entirely saturated by the zero modes of the field
b
,

\displaystyle\langle b(z_{1})\cdots b(z_{\Upsilon})\rangle={\vartheta[\delta](\zeta|\Omega)\over Z}\prod_{a<b}E(z_{a},z_{b})\prod_{a}\sigma(z_{a})

(D.7.18)

\bullet
Bibliographical notes

Classic introductions to Siegel modular forms and higher rank modular geometry may be found in the books by Siegel
[
347
,
348
]
, Igusa
[
346
]
, and Klingen
[
349
]
. Useful lecture notes on higher rank modular forms are those by van der Geer
[
350
]
. The connection between higher rank
\vartheta
-functions and Riemann surfaces is spelled out in the classic book by Fay
[
341
]
. The connection between higher rank
\vartheta
-functions and non-linear equations may be found in the survey article by Dubrovin
[
351
]
. The solution of the
bc
quantum system in terms of
\vartheta
-functions, using the work of
[
341
]
, was given in
[
60
]
.
